My Dear Doctor,

Thirteen months ago—give or take a few weeks, depending on how one counts the days of near-death and mild regret—I found myself in a state of such profound repose that I began to suspect the story had reached its final chapter. A kind soul (who shall remain nameless, though I suspect he was the same fellow who once tried to sell me a goat for my birthday) brought you to my bedside, where I lay like a particularly unimpressive statue in a museum of illness.

Now, while I may not have been at death’s door exactly—more like leaning against it with a cup of tea and a mildly apologetic expression—your constant vigilance, your soothing manner, and your uncanny ability to diagnose things without actually looking at me were nothing short of miraculous. You didn’t just save my life; you saved my dignity, which, let’s be honest, was hanging by a thread thinner than a surgeon’s needle.

And so, in lieu of payment—which you refused with the grace of a man who knows his worth but also understands the value of a good joke—I offer this humble tribute: thanks. Not just for me, but for my family, who endured my dramatics with remarkable patience (and occasional eye-rolling).

You’ve earned your place in the annals of medical heroism—and possibly in the next volume of *The History of Pendennis*, if I ever get around to writing it.

Yours most sincerely and gratefully,

P.S. If you ever need a patient who’s willing to complain about everything but still pay in praise, I’m available. Just don’t expect me to wear a nightcap again.

If this book—two years’ worth of scribbling, now dumped unceremoniously into the public’s lap—fails in artistry (and let’s be honest, it probably does), at least it has the virtue of being somewhat truthful. That’s more than you can say for most works that spend months getting polished into something so perfect they might as well be made of porcelain. Here, there’s no pretense: the writer talks straight to you, like a slightly tipsy acquaintance at a dinner party who just can’t stop rambling about his childhood cat. Inevitably, he stumbles over words, says things he later regrets, and occasionally spills tea on the manuscript. But that’s the charm! It’s a conversation between two people—one with a pen, one with a mind—and conversations, as we know, are rarely flawless.

And because this author is constantly babbling away, he inevitably reveals himself: his quirks, his vanities, his inexplicable obsession with cats (true story). You don’t judge a man by a single sentence or a fleeting mood. You get to know him through the long, messy, often boring course of daily interaction. Likewise, if you’ve read this far, you’re entitled to ask: Is this fellow honest? Does he mean what he says? Or is he just another fraud, pretending to feel deep emotions while secretly calculating how many readers will cry at page 127?

I can’t pretend I didn’t luck out. This book has found more readers than I ever dreamed possible. And while I can’t demand that you stay awake during every chapter (though I do hope you try), I *do* ask you to believe that I’m not trying to deceive you. I’m not here to sell you lies wrapped in poetic language. If truth isn’t present, then what’s the point? There’s nothing worse than a novelist who fakes sentiment like a bad actor in a melodrama.

Now, for those of you who crave “excitement” (you know who you are), here’s a little secret: this book started with a much grittier plan. We were going to have a hardened criminal in St. Giles’s—complete with noble qualities—being regularly visited by a young lady from Belgravia. Imagine the drama! The slang! The fashionably awkward dialogue! The battles! The murders! I even had Colonel Altamont scheduled for execution—until nine o’clock this morning, when I suddenly felt sorry for him and spared his life. He was literally at the window, ready to meet his fate, and I said, “Wait! Let’s talk!” The publishers were remarkably patient about all this, though I suspect they were quietly hoping I’d go back to writing something less… emotionally taxing.

But I abandoned the plan. Why? Because I realized I couldn’t pull it off. I’ve never been close to a convict in my life. I’ve never spent time in a jailhouse or learned the lingo of ruffians. So instead of trying to compete with M. Eugène Sue (who clearly knows his way around a prison yard), I decided to write about something I actually understand: ordinary people, doing ordinary things, sometimes failing, sometimes succeeding, but always breathing real air.

Even our respectable gentlemen—yes, these characters too—are shown as they really are: flawed, selfish, educated, and occasionally ridiculous. Since Tom Jones died, no one’s dared to show a man in full, unvarnished glory. We must wrap them in velvet, give them polite smiles, and pretend they don’t have dirty thoughts. Society won’t tolerate the raw truth. I’ve already lost subscribers—ladies, mostly—who took offense when I described a young man resisting temptation. My goal wasn’t to shock; it was to show that he *felt* the temptation, and that he *chose* to resist it. That’s courage. That’s humanity.

You won’t hear much about what really goes on in clubs, colleges, or mess-rooms—the real talk, the real lives of your sons. This book tries to be a little more honest than usual. Not because I’m trying to be scandalous, but because I believe truth, however uncomfortable, is better than fiction.

So here’s to truth. Whether you’re sitting in a lecture hall or curled up with a cup of tea, the message remains the same: honesty is the only thing that matters. Even if it’s not always pleasant.

KENSINGTON, Nov. 26th, 1850

It was one of those bright, bustling mornings in London’s high season when the city seemed to have polished itself up for a grand performance—every cab horse shined, every gentleman’s hat sat at just the right angle, and even the pigeons looked as if they’d been dressed by a French tailor. Into this glittering world strode Major Arthur Pendennis, the very picture of military elegance in retirement. He came from his lodgings like a man who knew exactly where he belonged—and that place was not merely at the Club in Pall Mall, but *on* it.

Now, the Club wasn’t just any club. It was a temple of taste, wit, and fine wine—where only the most distinguished men could be seen without drawing suspicion. And Pendennis? He wasn’t just a member. He was its unofficial headmaster, chief tastemaker, and occasional disciplinarian. The waiters bowed to him like courtiers before a king. The stewards tiptoed around him as if he might sneeze and bring down the entire establishment. His presence was so perfectly timed—always arriving at ten minutes past ten—that you could set your watch by his boots, which were blacker than midnight and shone with the intensity of a newly minted penny.

He wore a checked cravat that never frayed until dinner (a feat even the gods envied), a buff waistcoat adorned with buttons bearing the crown of England (because why wear a button unless it has royal endorsement?), and linen so crisp and white that Mr. Brummel himself—had he still been allowed into England—would’ve offered his last guinea for the name of her laundress.

His coat? Flawless. His gloves? Whiter than snow on a winter morning. His cane? A walking stick that probably had a pedigree. Even his whiskers were arranged with military precision, like two well-drilled battalions of facial hair.

From afar, he looked about thirty. Up close? Ah, there was a little deception—a few crow’s-feet near the eyes, a hint of dye in the hair, and the faintest whisper of age beneath the perfect surface. But who cared? He carried himself like a man who had never lost a battle—or perhaps just hadn’t bothered to fight one yet.

And then there was the table. Not just *a* table. *The* table. By the fire, near the window, facing the door—his personal throne. No one dared sit there. Once or twice, some reckless young fool tried to claim it, but the moment Pendennis took the next seat and fixed them with a look that said, “I know what you did last summer,” they fled faster than a hare chased by a pack of angry dukes.

His letters awaited him, neatly stacked like soldiers at attention. There were invitations—dinner cards thick as bricks, promising three courses and endless conversation about politics, poetry, and whether Lady So-and-So really *was* engaged to Lord Something-or-Other. There were notes from marchionesses, bishops, and marquises—all seeking advice, company, or simply a word of wisdom from the man whose opinion mattered more than a coronation speech.

Pendennis read them all with the calm dignity of a man who had mastered both fashion and fate. He accepted the Bishop of Ealing (slow dinners were acceptable when they involved ecclesiastical robes). He declined Cutler, the East India Director (too much talk of tea and tariffs). He accepted Steyne’s invitation—French-speaking party at the Star and Garter, no less—but only after checking that Glowry, the Scotch surgeon across the room, was watching with jealous eyes.

Glowry, incidentally, had no invitations. None. Zero. Zilch. And while he sipped his weak tea and stared at Pendennis’s pile of correspondence, he muttered under his breath: “Is it another _Jook_?”—by which he meant, “Is it another woman writing to him in distress?” Because clearly, Pendennis’s life was nothing but a series of dramatic love letters, social obligations, and minor crises involving hats.

But among all these grand missives, one letter stood apart. Small. Plain. With a country postmark and a seal that looked like it had been stamped by a chicken. It bore the address: *Fairoaks, Clavering St. Mary’s*. And the handwriting—oh, the handwriting! Delicate, feminine, trembling slightly, as if the writer had been weeping over the inkwell.

“Immediate,” it said, underlined with a frantic dash.

Pendennis ignored it. For now. He had more important things to do—like deciding whether to wear his gold pocket-watch chain or his silver one today. But eventually, even the most powerful men must answer their rural relatives.

He broke the seal. “Fairoaks” was engraved upon it. And inside, he found two letters—one from his sister, Helen, and one from his nephew, Arthur Pendennis Jr.

The first began:
“My dear Major Pendennis, I beg and implore you to come to me immediately.”

Immediately? Oh, yes. Immediately was the word of the day.

“I am in the greatest grief and perplexity,” she wrote. “My dearest boy—our darling Arthur—has formed… I can hardly write it… a passion, an infatuation… for an actress performing here.”

Pendennis blinked. Then blinked again. Then dropped his toast.

An *actress*?

A *passion*?

For a woman who was *twelve years older*?

And the boy wanted to marry her?

“Hay!” muttered Glowry, half out loud. “What’s got into Pendennis now?”

The Major’s face went from composed to crimson to something resembling volcanic fury. He read on.

“She insists upon marrying her,” the letter continued. “Do come instantly. As Arthur’s guardian, entreat, command, the wretched child to give up this most deplorable resolution.”

And then, signed: *Your unhappy affectionate sister, Helen Pendennis.*

Pendennis put down the letter. Took a deep breath. Then picked up the second one—the one written in a shaky hand, sealed with wax that looked like it had been applied during a seizure.

It began:
“FAIROAKS, Monday, Midnight.”

Ah. Midnight. That explained the urgency.

“My dear Uncle,” the boy wrote, “In informing you of my engagement with Miss Costigan…”

Miss Costigan? Of course. The famous Miss Fotheringay of Drury Lane and Crow Street. The tragedian whose voice could make angels weep and critics sigh.

“And though I know society may not approve,” the letter continued, “her family is as ancient and noble as our own!”

Yes, because nothing says nobility like being descended from kings of Ireland. Especially when your ancestor was a king *before* the British Empire existed. Useful fact, that.

“Although Miss Costigan is some years older than myself,” the boy added, “that does not operate as a barrier to my affection.”

No, indeed. Nothing stops true love—not age, not class, not common sense.

“I have loved once,” he declared, “and I shall die without ever knowing another passion.”

Oh, the drama. The pathos. The sheer *audacity* of it all.

Pendennis read the rest—how the boy begged him to come down, reconcile the mother, bless the union, and generally save the family from ruin. And then, the kicker:
“I press for a speedy marriage with my Emily—for why, in truth, should it be delayed? A delay implies a doubt, which I cast from me as unworthy.”

Unworthy? Unworthy of whom? Of *himself*, apparently. Or maybe of the entire concept of rational thought.

When Pendennis finished reading, he didn’t speak. He didn’t move. He just sat there, staring at the letter like it had just bit him.

Glowry, seeing this, reached into his card-case and felt for his lancet. Just in case. Just in case the Major needed medical intervention.

Because honestly? This was worse than a broken leg. This was a shattered reputation.

The head of the Pendennises—going to marry an actress? At *eighteen*? Who was twelve years older? And who happened to be a *tragedy queen*?

“Oh, mercy,” Pendennis groaned inwardly. “People will laugh. They’ll say, ‘There goes Pendennis—once the finest man in London, now reduced to escorting his nephew to a nuptial ceremony in a barn!’”

And worse—he’d have to cancel Steyne’s dinner. The Bishop’s. The Marquis’s. All of it. He’d have to take the mail-coach—tight, uncomfortable, smelling of damp wool and regret—instead of enjoying a leisurely evening with the cream of English society.

He rose from his table, left his breakfast untouched, and marched into the writing-room. There, with the solemnity of a man signing his own death warrant, he penned refusal after refusal.

To the Marquis: “Regretfully unable to attend.”
To the Earl: “Duty calls.”
To the Bishop: “Family matters of the utmost gravity.”

Then he sent his servant to book seats on the evening coach—charging the cost to the account of the widow and the young scapegrace he was supposed to be guarding.

As he walked back to his table, Glowry watched him with pity.

“Poor devil,” he murmured. “First love. Never good for breakfast.”

And indeed, it wasn’t. The toast had gone cold. The coffee had turned sour. And the future—well, the future looked like a long journey through the countryside, ending in a house full of emotional wreckage and a boy who believed in love like it was divine revelation.

And somewhere, in the depths of Fairoaks, Miss Fotheringay probably practiced her lines—because she was going to need them.

In the early days of George the Magnificent’s reign—when the world was still figuring out whether to wear cravats or just go with a scarf tied like a pirate—there lived in Clavering, a small town in the west of England, a gentleman named Pendennis. Now, this wasn’t some dashing nobleman who’d been born with a silver spoon and a title already engraved on his forehead. No, sir. This Pendennis had *earned* his dignity, one gritty apothecary appointment at a time.

Back in the day, when he was young and poor (and possibly not quite as dignified), Mr. Pendennis ran a little shop in Bath—a place so modest it might have been mistaken for a storage room for someone’s old boots. Above the door hung a gilt pestle and mortar, gleaming like a warning sign: “Do not enter unless you are prepared to be treated with both medicine and mild disdain.” Inside, Mr. Pendennis dispensed remedies to the sick, plasters to farmers’ wives, and toothbrushes to anyone who looked like they hadn’t brushed their teeth since the Regency began.

And yet! Despite selling toothpaste that probably tasted like regret and powdered chalk, this man was no mere tradesman. He was, in fact, a gentleman of ancient bloodline, whose family tree stretched back so far it likely included a Druid who once cursed the Romans for stealing his best acorn. The Pendennises were Cornish nobility—well, almost. They had intermarried with Normans (a move that, while socially questionable, at least gave them a decent excuse for being perpetually confused about their own heritage). They were related to every important family in Wales and Brittany—though, admittedly, only by marriage, a cousin’s cousin’s aunt’s husband’s dog, or something equally tenuous.

Pendennis himself had studied at Cambridge—briefly. He’d even managed to scrape through two years before his father, in a moment of financial recklessness, declared bankruptcy. That ended any dreams of academic glory. So, instead of becoming a scholar or a poet, young Pen found himself apprenticed to his mother’s brother—a London apothecary of such low birth that even the local pigeons avoided him. It was a fate worse than death, but necessity, as they say, is the mother of all awkward careers.

He eventually broke free from his uncle’s clutches (who, by the way, was more interested in mixing laxatives than in manners) and set up shop in Bath under his own name. His early days were lean—so lean, in fact, that he once considered selling his mother’s hairbrush to pay for a new windowpane. But then came Lady Ribstone, a woman of considerable size, wealth, and temper, who was knocked sideways by an Irish chairman driving a carriage like a man possessed by the spirit of drunkenness itself.

The accident happened right outside Pendennis’s shop. The chairpole speared through the most beautiful pink bottle in the window (a tragedy!), and Lady Ribstone—screaming like a startled badger—collapsed into the doorway. Mr. Pendennis, ever the gentleman, rushed forward with cinnamon and sal-volatile, revived her, and treated her with such soothing grace that she immediately appointed him her personal apothecary. And thus, by sheer luck and excellent bedside manner, Pendennis became the medical man to one of Somerset’s most prominent families.

From there, things improved rapidly. He treated Master Ribstone during a Christmas fever (which involved a lot of hot baths and very little sleep), won over the entire Codlingbury household, and soon found himself patronized by the upper crust of Bath society. His shop, once humble, became stylish; he stopped selling perfumes and toothbrushes (they were “unworthy of a gentleman of ancient lineage,” he declared, though he’d once sold a tube of toothpaste to a goat); he shut the shop entirely and opened a proper surgery attended by a young man who wore a waistcoat and tried very hard not to look like a servant.

Then came the gig—with a man to drive it. Then a one-horse carriage. And finally, just before his mother passed away, he bought a close carriage with the Pendennis coat of arms emblazoned on the panels—proudly, defiantly, like a statement: *I am now a man of consequence.*

“Would Arthur say now?” his mother asked, gazing from her bedroom window, where her chair had been rolled to catch a glimpse of the grand vehicle. “He never once came to see Johnny through all these years of poverty.”

“Captain Pendennis is in India, mother,” said John, adjusting his collar. “And if you please, I wish you would not call me Johnny in front of Mr. Parkins.”

She died soon after. Her voice—the kind that scolded, cajoled, and occasionally sang off-key hymns—was gone. Pendennis changed the night-bell to her room and slept in her big bed, which smelled faintly of lavender and unresolved grievances.

By this time, he was well past forty, and had achieved what every self-respecting provincial doctor dreams of: *being a gentleman.* He’d framed his pedigree and hung it proudly in the drawing room, between a painting of Codlingbury House and another of St. Boniface’s College, Cambridge—where he’d spent exactly two months before being forced into the world of medicinal mixtures and moral compromise.

Around the same time, another patient died—Lady Pontypool, a woman of many marriages, including one to a reverend who apparently believed in the power of divine punishment. She’d been cared for by Miss Helen Thistlewood, a distant relative of the Bareacres family and daughter of a naval officer killed at Copenhagen. Miss Thistlewood was quiet, sweet, and endured her elderly patroness’s tyranny with the patience of a saint—and perhaps a touch of masochism.

It was at Lady Pontypool’s funeral that Pendennis, feeling a sudden surge of courage (or possibly just a strong desire to avoid being alone), decided to ask Miss Thistlewood to marry him. He’d rehearsed the speech in bed, then perfected it in his carriage, whispering lines like, “I am old, yes, but I am good-natured, respectable, and possess a certain amount of property.” He held her hand—cold, pale, and trembling—as he delivered it.

She blushed, curtsied deeply, and said she would “consider” his proposal.

They married during the dull season in Bath—when London was in full swing. Pendennis, having secured lodgings in Holles Street via a professional friend (M.R.C.S., naturally), took his new wife to the theatres, parks, and Chapel Royal. He left cards on Lord Pontypool, the Earl of Bareacres, and Sir Pepin Ribstone—only to receive silence, a polite visit, and a card delivered by a footman, respectively. But the Ribstones invited them to dinner—an event Pendennis bragged about until the day he died.

His dream had always been to become a gentleman. And now, thanks to frugality, a lucky copper mine investment, and a well-timed sale of his apothecary business, he was officially a landowner. He bought a house and small estate near Clavering, where he could walk across fields and pretend he was the lord of the manor. He retired from medicine, sold the shop to Mr. Parkins (who promptly renamed it “Parkins & Sons”), and adopted the title of Squire—much to the delight of everyone except the local postmaster, who thought it was all rather excessive.

Arthur Pendennis, his son, was eight at the time. He remembered nothing of the shop, the pills, or the smell of turpentine. His father never spoke of it. Instead, he wore a bottle-green coat with brass buttons, attended market in a knowing manner, and bowed gravely to coachmen as if he’d been born to it.

He founded the Clavering Book Club (mostly to impress people), started the Samaritan Soup and Blanket Society (to show charity), and rerouted the mail coach away from Cacklefield and through Clavering (because, clearly, Clavering deserved better).

At church, he was a model vestryman. At market, he inspected oats, punched geese in the breast, and negotiated with farmers like a man who’d seen everything. He was now ashamed to be called “Doctor”—that title was for common folk. Everyone who wanted to please him called him “Squire.”

And then there were the portraits. A whole gallery of them appeared overnight—Lelys, Vandykes, and one suspiciously similar to a portrait of Oliver Cromwell, but with different eyes. Pendennis insisted they were ancestors. His wife looked skeptical. His son believed every word. To little Arthur, Roger Pendennis of Agincourt, Arthur Pendennis of Crecy, and General Pendennis of Blenheim were real men—just as real as Robinson Crusoe or Peter Wilkins. In fact, he often argued that the Pendennises were more heroic, because they had actual coats of arms.

Their income—about £800 a year—wasn’t enough to live among the great folks of the county, but it was enough to live near them. They dined each other twice a year under moonlight, sometimes traveling twelve miles for a meal that cost half a crown. They also had the pleasure of hosting Mrs. Pybus, who constantly poked around Helen’s conservatories and intercepted soup tickets. Captain Glanders, a swaggering dragoon officer, was forever trying to get the Squire involved in feuds with the Vicar, the Postmaster, and the Reverend F. Wapshot—whose son, Anglesea Glanders, had allegedly been flogged too hard for wearing mismatched socks.

Fairoaks, their home, stood a mile from Clavering, overlooking the river Brawl. On the other side lay Clavering Park—the decaying estate of Sir Francis Clavering, Bart. The house was empty, shuttered, and haunted by the ghosts of wasted fortune. Arthur used to wander through its halls, imagining the spirits of long-dead nobles whispering secrets. At sunset, the view was magnificent—the park glowed golden, the river ran off into shadow, and the towers of Clavering Abbey rose in purple splendor. Arthur would stand with his mother, recite poetry (“These are thy glorious works, Parent of Good…”), and they’d hug like they were auditioning for a sentimental novel.

But when Mr. Pendennis entered the room, all poetry ceased. He walked in with his newspaper under his arm, his hat perfectly brushed, his meals served at precisely six o’clock, and his nap scheduled for 6:15 sharp. He read the *Gardener’s Gazette*, played picquet, and inspected the stables, henhouses, and pigstye—all at exact times. Little Pen, meanwhile, buried himself in books, reading anything from *Robinson Crusoe* to the *Encyclopædia Britannica*.

Every two years, Major Pendennis—the elder brother—would visit from India. He was the family’s connection to high society. He brought news of lords, dined with dukes, and spoke of them with soldierly respect. “My Lord Bareacres has kindly invited me to shoot pheasants,” he’d say, as if it were a diplomatic mission. The Major was a bachelor, despite offers from Miss Hunkle of Lilybank, who had fifteen hundred pounds a year and a reputation for being “very agreeable.” But the Major declined. “As a bachelor, nobody cares how poor I am,” he explained. “Miss Hunkle, though respectable, lacks the birth and manners required for my sphere. I shall die an old bachelor.”

Time proved him right. Miss Hunkle married a French nobleman and became Baroness de Carambole—though she divorced him within a year, claiming he kept trying to teach her how to ride a horse without using a saddle.

The Major was beloved at Fairoaks. He praised Mrs. Pendennis as “the finest lady in England,” and truly meant it. She was gentle, kind, and so perfect that country ladies with three times her money couldn’t help but resent her. Miss Pybus called her cold. Miss Pierce said she was proud. Mrs. Wapshot, as a doctor of divinity’s wife, felt she should be given the *pas*. But Mrs. Pendennis paid no attention. She prayed, loved her family, helped neighbors, and carried herself with the calm dignity of someone who knew she was doing the right thing—even if no one else did.

Arthur, growing up under such a mother, thought of her as an angel—perhaps slightly less supernatural than the Virgin Mary, but definitely more practical. When his father took her to balls or concerts, he’d walk in with her on his arm and stare boldly at the nobility, as if to say: *Look at her. Can any of you produce a woman like that?*

And really, could they?

Not that it mattered much. For Arthur Pendennis, the future was wide open. His father was a gentleman. His mother was an angel. And somewhere, in the distance, the Major was planning his next visit—and possibly another failed romance. Life, in all its absurdity and charm, had begun.

Arthur Pendennis was about sixteen when he began to rule his own little kingdom—Fairoaks, that is, with its stables, its horses, and his mother’s undivided attention. He wasn’t tall by any means, standing at five feet six, but then he had the audacity to grow another two inches before he’d even hit eighteen. That made him three inches taller than his father, which caused quite a stir in the family. “Could it be?” Mrs. Pendennis would whisper to herself, staring up at her son like he’d just sprouted wings. “Could any man possibly be three inches taller than Mr. Pendennis?” The answer, of course, was yes—but only if you were Arthur.

Physically speaking, Arthur had what his friends called a "dumpy" figure, though his mother insisted on calling it "neat." His hair was a healthy brown that glowed like gold under the sun (which, given how often he wore his hat, didn’t happen much). His face was round, rosy, freckled, and perpetually smiling—like a cheerful potato with blue eyes. As for whiskers? Oh, those! He dreamed of them, sighed over them, wrote poetry about them. When they finally came in, they turned out to be a fiery shade of red—more ginger ale than gentlemanly stubble. Still, no one could deny he had a face that radiated warmth, honesty, and a kind of innocent mischief that made people want to hug him, feed him cake, or both.

He never went back to school after leaving Greyfriars—not because he couldn’t, but because he didn’t want to. The discipline there had been too strict, the masters too severe, and the boys too prone to throw inkwells at each other during Latin class. His uncle suggested a return, but Pen skillfully hinted to his mother that Greyfriars was full of “sad wild fellows” who spent their time smoking, fighting, and occasionally stealing the headmaster’s wig. His mother, already trembling at the thought of such moral peril, agreed immediately: Arthur would stay home. And so he did—much to the dismay of his uncle, who later lamented that he’d lost the chance to mold a future statesman.

Then came the great military dream. Pen’s uncle, being well-connected (and slightly vain), offered to petition His Royal Highness the Commander-in-Chief for a commission in the Foot Guards. Pen nearly fainted with joy. He’d once seen Tom Ricketts—the boy from the fourth form whose trousers were so tight they looked like sausage casings—marching past St. James’s Palace in crimson and gold, wearing a bear-skin cap that looked like it belonged on a grizzly bear. Tom had nodded at him patronizingly, as if to say, “Yes, I’m now a soldier. You’re not. But I used to get beat up by you.” That moment haunted Pen. He imagined himself riding through London, epaulettes flapping, bayonets gleaming, cymbals crashing, while ladies waved handkerchiefs and men shouted, “Huzzah!”

But then his mother stepped in.

“Now, Arthur,” she said, clasping her hands together with solemn gravity, “I don’t quarrel with others who think differently—but in my opinion, a Christian has no right to make the army a profession. Mr. Pendennis never, never would have allowed it. And I should be very unhappy if you considered it.”

Pen, bless him, was more devoted to his mother’s happiness than to his own dreams. He wouldn’t have cut off his nose and ears to please her—but he *would* give up his entire fantasy of wearing a scarlet coat and marching in step with the King’s guards. So he did. With a noble sigh, he surrendered his epaulettes to the winds, along with his ambition, his glory, and his hope of ever being mistaken for a real hero.

His mother declared him the noblest creature in existence. His uncle, however, wrote a curt letter saying he thought Arthur was “rather a spooney”—which, in diplomatic terms, meant “a complete fool.”

But Arthur wasn’t entirely idle. At Christmas, when Major Pendennis came down to Fairoaks, Pen proved himself a capable rider. He had a mare named Rebecca (after Isaac’s daughter, naturally) and rode her with courage, grace, and just enough flair to impress without showing off. He jumped fences like a man possessed, yet kept his cool. He wrote letters to his old school chums describing his boots, his rides, and the glorious thrill of galloping across the countryside. He even started thinking seriously about getting a scarlet coat—though only in secret, and only when his mother wasn’t watching.

And yet, despite all this horseplay, he wasn’t *entirely* neglecting his studies. He read everything he could lay hands on—except what was required at school. Those books he avoided like plague. But anything else? He devoured it. From Inchbald’s Theatre to White’s Farriery, from French novels found in Clavering to ancient texts perched atop Doctor Portman’s library steps—he read them all. He’d sit on the highest rung of the book ladder, knees bent, a folio balanced on his lap, reading Hakluyt’s Travels, Hobbes’s Leviathan, Augustini Opera, or Chaucer’s Poems with the same intensity as a man trying to escape a burning house.

He was especially fond of the Vicar, Dr. Portman, who introduced him to port wine—a taste that stayed with him for life. And poor Mrs. Portman, who adored Arthur like a son, was secretly heartbroken that her daughter Minny was too old for him. Miss Myra Portman was, at that time, two years older than Arthur’s mother and weighed as much as Arthur and his mother combined. A match, clearly, was impossible.

Are these details boring? Perhaps. But look back, dear reader, to your own youth. How many memories do you have of reading by candlelight, dreaming of grand adventures, feeling the world was yours for the taking? Arthur Pendennis lived in such a time. Brave, gentle, warm-hearted, and looking at life with honest, hopeful eyes. It’s only when we’re grown that we realize how precious those days were—and how quickly they vanish.

To keep Arthur from becoming completely idle (as the Cistercian doctor had warned), Mr. Smirke, Dr. Portman’s curate, was hired to tutor him. Smirke was a man of impeccable manners at tea parties, with a curl on his forehead and a neckcloth tied with melancholy elegance. He was also a decent scholar and mathematician—though Arthur learned very little from him. Why? Because Smirke, when riding into the courtyard on his pony, turned out his toes so absurdly and left such a gap between his knees and the saddle that Arthur couldn’t help laughing. One day, Arthur put Smirke on Rebecca and took him on a ride over a common where the county fox-hounds happened to be hunting. Smirke, astride the mare, terrified the hounds, lamed one by riding directly into the pack, and received a lecture from the huntsman so colorful it made Smirke wish he’d never left Oxford.

Smirke confided his poems—both Latin and English—to Arthur. He presented Mrs. Pendennis with a printed volume of his verses from Clapham. Together, they read the ancient poets at a rapid pace—nothing like the slow, methodical grind of the Cistercians. Arthur preferred speed. If Smirke got stuck, Arthur would jump in and translate, racing through the Iliad, the Odyssey, the tragedies, and even Aristophanes—whom Arthur declared “the greatest poet of all.” Unfortunately, he forgot most of it later, remembering only fragments—like a man who quotes Shakespeare in Parliament but doesn’t know the plays.

He loved Byron and Moore, though Smirke sighed sadly at both. Arthur, however, was a true fire-worshipper. He knew Byron by heart and would recite lines like “Zuleika, I am not thy brother” to little Laura, who sat sewing at her mother’s knee, wide-eyed and utterly confused. She listened, wordless, as Arthur read aloud—Shakespeare, Byron, Pope, Lalla Rookh—though she understood none of it.

Mrs. Pendennis melted when he read Bishop Heber or Mrs. Hemans. Her tears flowed freely, and she’d hide her face in her handkerchief. The *Christian Year* became a sacred text between mother and son—read softly, reverently, rarely repeated. Arthur always remembered those moments: walking across fields on Sundays, church bells ringing, hope filling his heart, doubt nowhere to be found.

It was during this time that Arthur published his first poems in the *County Chronicle*, signed “NEP.” They included “To a Tear,” “On the Anniversary of the Battle of Waterloo,” “To Madame Caradori singing at the Assize Meetings,” and “On Saint Bartholomew’s Day”—a fierce anti-Catholic rant that probably frightened the local priest. These poems, along with his first socks and hair clippings, are still preserved by Mrs. Pendennis, no doubt tucked away in a cedar chest beside his baby bottle.

Arthur would gallop Rebecca across the Dumpling Downs or into Chatteris (which, for the sake of this tale, we shall call Chatteris), reciting his own poetry with dramatic flair. He believed himself possessed by a Byronic spirit—though in truth, he was mostly just excited.

His poetic genius leaned toward the gloomy. He once brought his mother a tragedy in which sixteen people died before the second act. She laughed so hard he threw the manuscript into the fire in a fit of pique. He planned an epic poem about Cortez and the Inca’s Daughter, wrote part of *Seneca, or the Fatal Bath*, and *Ariadne in Naxos*—all with choruses and strophes that baffled Mrs. Pendennis. He also began a *History of the Jesuits*, attacking them with fervor and warning Protestants of their “machinations.” His loyalty to Church and King warmed his mother’s heart.

At election time, when Sir Giles Beanfield stood against Lord Trehawk (a Whig and friend of Popery), Arthur rode alongside Dr. Portman on his grey mare Dowdy, leading the Clavering voters. He wore a blue ribbon on Rebecca and a bow of immense pride—made by his mother. He gave his first speech at the Blue Hotel—and also, for the first time in his life, drank more wine than was good for him.

When he returned late at night, lanterns flickered in the yard, servants gathered, and half a dozen farmers followed him, shouting the Blue song. He invited them all in for Madeira—“some very good Madeira, John, go fetch some!”—but before things could get out of hand, Mrs. Pendennis appeared in a white wrapper, candle in hand. The sight of her pale, beautiful face silenced the crowd. They touched their hats and rode off.

And then came love.

Ah, love—the great business of youth, as the poets say. Arthur felt it stirring within him. He sighed, opened his chest, and whispered, “Aura, veni.” Who among us hasn’t courted some phantom mistress in our youth?

He needed a muse—a real girl to write poems for, someone to adore instead of fictional Zuleikas and Ianthes. He read Waller, Dryden, Prior, translated Anacreon, and called upon Alma Venus, delight of gods and men. Smirke, meanwhile, was equally enamored—though not of girls, but of Mrs. Pendennis.

The poor curate was madly in love—though “madly” is generous. He was smitten, gently, quietly, desperately. He gave Arthur his Elzevir Horace, a Greek Testament from his mother, a silver pencil-case. He let Arthur skip lessons, read whatever he wanted, and often seemed ready to confess his feelings. But when Arthur asked, “Tell us, old chap, is she handsome? Blue eyes or black?” Smirke would sigh, gaze upward, and change the subject.

Once, when Mrs. Pendennis visited Clavering, Smirke sent for pound-cakes instantly. The sofa she sat on became sacred. The glass she drank from? He kept flowers in it forever after.

Meanwhile, Arthur’s mother, never tired of hearing about her son, found Mr. Smirke increasingly charming. She praised Arthur’s bravery, his generosity, his riding skills—everything. And she began to save money for him, cutting back on household expenses, hiding the silver dish-covers, and serving meals that resembled monkish fare. She was becoming miserly—for Arthur’s future, of course.

But there was no young woman nearby worthy of Arthur’s affections. Dolly the dairymaid? Too low. Molly the blacksmith’s daughter? Too vulgar. Miss Minny Portman? Too old and too fond of Rollin’s Ancient History. The Boardback girls? Too London. Captain Glanders’s daughters? Still in pinafores, their plaits tied with pink ribbons. Arthur, lacking dance lessons, avoided assemblies. He rode every day, searching for Dulcinea—peering into carriages, hoping to see her in a yellow postchaise or a gig with three girls in beaver bonnets.

But the fairy princess never appeared.

He kept these dreams to himself. Every young soul has a secret world. Let it be. Don’t pry. Actaeon was a brute for spying on Diana. Leave your poet alone. Even your best advice can be a bore.

Mrs. Pendennis, however, sensed everything. She didn’t speak of it, but she knew. And she had already decided: Arthur would marry little Laura—when she was eighteen, he was twenty-six, and he had completed his grand tour, become a famous barrister, or settled in a sweet country parsonage surrounded by hollyhocks and roses.

One day, Arthur rode into Chatteris to deliver a poem to the *County Chronicle*. He stopped at the George Hotel, where the hostler was fussing over Rebecca, when a grand black tandem rattled into the yard. The driver yelled, “Hallo, Pendennis, is that you?” in a loud, patronizing tone.

Arthur blinked. Under the broad-brimmed hat and enormous greatcoat, he recognized his old schoolmate—Mr. Foker.

After returning to the George, Mr. Foker and his guest settled into a sumptuous meal in the coffee-room, where Mr. Rincer arrived with the first course, bowing so solemnly you’d think he was serving the Lord-Lieutenant himself. Foker attacked the turtle and venison with the same gusto he’d once shown for ginger-beer and smuggled polonies—though back then, “gusto” had meant surviving on half a loaf and a stolen apple. Pen couldn’t help but admire his refined taste when Foker declared the champagne “condemned gooseberry,” winked at the port with one eye, and announced it was “the right sort.” He even told the waiters there was no fooling him—he knew them all by their Christian names, asked after their children, and treated them like family.

As the London coaches clattered up—the old days being full of such dramatic arrivals—Foker flung open the window and called out to the guards and coachmen by name, asking about their families and mimicking the horn-blowing of Jem the ostler with astonishing accuracy. The horses’ cloths were whipped off, the carriages rattled away, and Foker turned to Pen with a satisfied grin: “A bottle of sherry, a bottle of sham, a bottle of port, and a shass caffy—it ain’t so bad, hay, Pen?” After demolishing all that, plus a mountain of nuts and fruit, Foker pronounced it time to “toddle.”

Pen sprang up, eyes bright, face flushed, and they headed toward the theatre. There, they paid the wheezy old lady dozing in the money-taker’s box—Mrs. Dropsicum, Bingley’s mother-in-law, famed for her Lady Macbeth. Foker knew her well. They nearly had first pick of seats in the boxes, though the theatre wasn’t exactly packed—despite Bingley’s grandiose playbills promising “a universal burst of attraction and galvanic thrills of delight.” A few scattered souls dotted the pit benches; others kicked and whistled from the galleries; and a dozen free-admission types occupied the boxes where our young gentlemen sat.

In a private box nearby, Lieutenants Rodgers and Podgers, along with young Cornet Tidmus of the Dragoons, lounged like kings. They didn’t seem to care much about the play—they chatted with the actors mid-scene and applauded by name, as if they were running the show themselves.

Bingley, the manager, played every major role—except when he modestly stepped aside for visiting London stars (who occasionally graced Chatteris). Tonight, he was the ‘Stranger,’ dressed in tight pantaloons, Hessian boots, a long cloak, and a drooping beaver hat adorned with a hearse feather. His raddled face barely peeked out from beneath a great buckled brown wig. He wore stage jewelry too—especially a massive, shiny ring on his little finger, which he twirled dramatically in front of the audience like a magician showing off a trick coin. It had once belonged to George Frederick Cooke, who got it from Mr. Quin, who probably bought it for a shilling. Bingley believed the world was hypnotized by its sparkle. He also liked to read from the stage-book—a book that looked nothing like a real book. It was rouged, tawdry, held like a sacred relic, pointed at ominously, and waved heavenward as if divine inspiration flowed from its pages.

Anybody who’s seen a great light comedian—say, X.—in a chintz dressing-gown, pretending to be a nobleman reading in his chambers until Sir Harry arrives or father comes down for breakfast—anybody who’s seen this man over a fake book knows what true theatrical magic is. And now, the Stranger appeared, lying on the stage bank, holding his gilt volume, displaying his ring and Hessians like trophies. He saw Pen and immediately began acting *at* him.

“Again reading,” said Francis, his servant, “from morn to night. To him nature has no beauty—life no charm. For three years I have never seen him smile.” Bingley’s face darkened like a storm cloud.

Francis continued: “Nothing diverts him. O, if he would but attach himself to any living thing, were it an animal—for something man must love.”

Enter Tobias (played by Goll), emerging from the hut. “O, how refreshing, after seven long weeks, to feel these warm sunbeams once again. Thanks, bounteous heaven, for the joy I taste!” He pressed his cap between his hands, looked up, and prayed.

The Stranger watched him closely.

Francis: “This old man’s share of earthly happiness can be but little. Yet mark how grateful he is for his portion of it.”

Bingley: “Because though old, he is but a child in the leading-string of hope.”

(He stared hard at Foker, who merely sucked the top of his cane, unfazed.)

Francis: “Hope is the nurse of life.”

Bingley: “And her cradle—is the grave.”

He delivered this line with the mournful wail of a bassoon, fixing his gaze on Pendennis so intensely that the poor lad felt the whole house was staring at him. He dropped his eyes. When he raised them again, Bingley was still watching. All through the scene, the manager played directly to Pen, as if determined to conquer him.

When the Stranger prepared to do a good deed—sending Francis off with his book so the servant wouldn’t witness it—Bingley carefully marked the page, so he could continue reading offstage if he wished. But he did it all in plain sight of Pen, as if daring him to resist the spell.

At last, the scene ended. Foker tapped his cane and shouted, “Bravo, Bingley!”

“Give him a hand, Pendennis,” he said. “You know every chap likes a hand.”

Pen, laughing, joined in. The dragoons clapped furiously. You’d have thought the place was packed.

Then came the chamber in Wintersen Castle—Tobias’s hut, the Stranger’s boots, and servants bustling about. “That’s Hicks and Miss Thackthwaite,” whispered Foker. “Pretty girl, ain’t she, Pendennis? But stop—hurray—bravo! Here’s the Fotheringay!”

The pit erupted. Umbrellas thumped. The gallery fired a volley of applause. The dragoons and Foker clapped like mad. You’d swear the theatre was full.

Mr. Costigan’s red face and ragged whiskers peeked from the side-scene. Then—Mrs. Haller entered, head down, but lifting her eyes at the sound of the cheers, sweeping the house with a grateful glance, folding her hands across her chest, and sinking into a magnificent curtsey.

More applause. More umbrellas. Pen, flushed with wine and excitement, clapped louder than anyone—and sang “Bravo!” at the top of his lungs.

Mrs. Haller saw him. Everyone else saw him. Old Mr. Bows, the little first fiddler (whose orchestra was tonight bolstered by a detachment of the Dragoons, courtesy of Colonel Swallowtail), looked up from his desk, crutch beside him, and smiled at the boy’s enthusiasm.

Now, those who’ve only seen Miss Fotheringay since her marriage and entry into London society have no idea how stunning she was when Pen first laid eyes on her. And I warn my reader: the illustrator of this work (who draws ugly faces fairly well but utterly fails at beauty) cannot begin to capture her.

She was tall—six feet of grace and fire—at twenty-six (though she swore she was nineteen). Her forehead was vast, her black hair waved naturally over it (a style modern beauties try to imitate with crimping irons), braided into shining volumes at the back of a neck like the Louvre Venus’s. Her eyes—when she lifted them—shone with tenderness and mystery. Love and Genius seemed to look out, then shyly retreat, as if embarrassed to be seen at the lattice.

Who could have such a commanding brow but a woman of sharp mind? She never laughed (her teeth weren’t good), but a sweet, endless smile played around her lips, dimpling her cheeks and chin. Her nose defied description. Her ears were like tiny pearl shells—so delicate that even the most beautiful earrings in the theatre seemed to insult them.

She wore long black robes, flowing and graceful, which she swept with such elegance that you only glimpsed her sandals now and then—large, yes, but Pen thought them as magical as Cinderella’s slippers.

But it was her arms and hands that truly captivated. They surrounded her. When folded over her bosom in resignation, when dropped in silent agony, when raised in command, or fluttering in gaiety—like snowy doves before Venus’s chariot—she beckoned, repelled, entreated, embraced. Not just one admirer, but the whole house. She rose to her, as the phrase went, as she curtsied and bowed, enchanting all.

She stood there—complete, radiant—as Pen stared.

“I say, Pen,” Foker whispered, “isn’t she a stunner?”

“Hush!” Pen replied. “She’s speaking.”

She began her lines in a deep, sweet voice. Those familiar with *The Stranger* know the dialogue isn’t particularly profound—no original thoughts, no poetic genius. In fact, calling it stupid wouldn’t be far wrong. No one talks like that in real life. If we meet fools, thank God they don’t use such absurdly fine words.

But the Stranger’s talk is sham—like his book, his wig, his bank, his ring—but within the nonsense runs a truth: love, children, forgiveness. And wherever that truth is spoken, the world listens and sympathizes.

Mrs. Haller delivered her part with smothered sorrow, gushing pathos. At first, as Count Wintersen’s housekeeper giving orders for dinner and beds, she spoke with calm despair. But when the servants were gone, she poured her grief into the audience—each person felt she was speaking just to them. Little Bows in the orchestra twitched, nodded, pointed—then, at the famous line: “I have a William too, if he be still alive—Ah, yes, if he be still alive. His little sisters, too! Why, Fancy, dost thou rack me so? Why dost thou image my poor children fainting in sickness, and crying to—to—their mum—um—other”—he buried his face in his blue cotton handkerchief after shouting “Bravo!”

The entire house was moved. Foker pulled out a yellow bandanna and wept piteously.

As for Pen—he was gone. He followed her every move. When she left the stage, the world vanished. Lights blurred, officers reeled. He watched her at the side-scene, where her father took off her shawl. When reconciliation came—when she threw herself onto Bingley’s shoulders, the children clinging to his knees, the Countess (Mrs. Bingley) and Baron Steinforth (played with spirit by Garbetts)—Pen saw only Fotheringay. The curtain fell like a funeral pall. He heard nothing of Bingley’s announcement for the next evening. He didn’t realize the house was calling for Miss Fotheringay. The manager didn’t seem to notice anyone else had caused the success.

Finally, he understood—grinned, and reappeared with Mrs. Haller on his arm.

How beautiful she looked! Her hair had fallen loose. Officers threw her flowers. She clutched them to her heart, brushed her hair back, smiled all around. Her eyes met Pen’s.

Down went the curtain again. She was gone.

Not a note of the overture blew by the brass band of the Dragoons—kind permission of Colonel Swallowtail.

“She’s a crusher, ain’t she now?” Foker asked.

Pen didn’t hear. He couldn’t speak. He didn’t know what he felt—only that it was overwhelming, maddening, delicious. A fever of wild joy and longing.

Then Rowkins and Miss Thackthwaite danced the double hornpipe. Foker delighted in it, just as he had in the tragedy. Pen didn’t care. He only remembered that she had been in that scene.

It was a mist before his eyes.

At the dance’s end, he checked his watch. Time to go.

“Hang it, stay for *The Bravo of the Battle-Axe*,” Foker urged. “Bingley’s splendid in it—he wears red tights and carries Mrs. B. over the Pine-bridge of the Cataract. Only she’s too heavy. It’s great fun—do stay.”

Pen glanced at the bill, hoping to see her name hidden among the cast. But no. She wasn’t listed.

He had to leave. A long ride home. He squeezed Foker’s hand—choking, unable to speak—and left the theatre.

He wandered wildly through the town, unaware of time. Then mounted at the George and rode homeward. Clavering clock struck one as he entered the yard at Fairoaks.

His mother might have been awake, but she only heard him dash into bed and pull the covers over his head.

Pen hadn’t been known for sleepless nights. But tonight, he sank into a deep, dreamless sleep.

Even in later years, when anxiety kept men awake, they often fell asleep anyway—only to be jolted awake by it later. “Come, my man,” Anxiety says, “no more laziness—you must wake up and talk.” Then they talk into the midnight.

But Pen wasn’t there yet. He slept soundly—until morning, when rooks cawed from the wood beyond his window.

And—just as his eyes opened—the beloved image was already in his mind.

“My dear boy,” he heard her say, “you were in a sound sleep, and I would not disturb you. But I have been close by your pillow all this while—and I don’t intend that you shall leave me. I am Love! I bring fever and passion, wild longing, maddening desire, restless craving. Many a long day I heard you calling for me—and now I am come.”

Was Pen frightened? Not at all. He didn’t know what was coming. It was pure pleasure, wild delight.

Just as, three years earlier, when entering the fifth form at the Cistercians, his father had given him a gold watch—which he’d taken from under his pillow the moment he woke, polishing it endlessly, hiding in corners to listen to its ticking—so now Pen exulted in his new delight. He felt for it in his waistcoat pocket, wound it up at night, hugged it at dawn.

By the way, that first watch was flashy but poorly made—it never worked right. After a while, he forgot it, then traded it for a better one.

But Pen felt suddenly older. He was in love—fully, completely, like the hero of the best romance ever written.

He told John to bring shaving water with confidence. He dressed in his finest clothes, descended to breakfast with a haughty air, patronizing his mother and little Laura, who had been practicing her music lesson for hours.

After he read the prayers (without hearing a word), Laura asked, “What was the play about?”

Pen laughed and refused to tell her. Better she didn’t know.

Then she asked why he was wearing his finest pin and new waistcoat.

Pen blushed and told his mother that his old schoolfellow, whom he’d dined with in Chatteris, was studying with a tutor in Baymouth—a very learned man. Since Pen was going to college, and several young men studied in Baymouth, he wanted to ride over and see what they were reading.

Laura frowned. Helen Pendennis stared at her son, troubled by the vague fear that had haunted her since Farmer Gurnett returned saying Pen wouldn’t come home for dinner.

Arthur’s eyes defied her. She tried to console herself. The boy had never lied to her.

Pen behaved with exaggerated dignity during breakfast. After taking leave of both ladies, he was soon heard riding out of the stable court.

At first, he rode slowly. Then, once out of earshot, he galloped like a madman.

Smirke, riding softly with his toes out, thinking of his own affairs, met his pupil—who shot past like a whirlwind.

Smirke’s pony shied. The gentle curate went flying over his head into the stinging-nettles.

Pen laughed, pointed toward Baymouth, and was half a mile away before Smirke picked himself up.

Pen had decided he must see Foker that morning—must hear about her, know about her, be near someone who knew her.

Meanwhile, honest Smirke, sitting amid nettles as his pony nibbled the hedge, wondered: should he go to Fairoaks now that his pupil had clearly fled?

Yes, he decided. He might ask Mrs. Pendennis when Arthur would return. And hear Miss Laura recite Watts’s Catechism.

He climbed back on the pony—both used to his slipping off—and advanced toward the house from which his scholar had just bolted in a frenzy.

Thus, love makes fools of us all—big and small. The curate tumbled headfirst in pursuit. And Pen had launched into the first heat of the mad race.

Rebecca the mare, galloping with all the dignity of a lady in a hurry, carried Pen straight into Baymouth, where he deposited her at the inn stables and dashed off to Mr. Foker’s lodgings—directions kindly provided by said gentleman the day before. The apartment turned out to be above a chemist’s shop that seemed to specialize in cigars and soda water, both of which were disappearing at an alarming rate thanks to the youthful clientele living overhead.

Pen arrived breathless and rosy-cheeked, looking like a man who’d just won a race and eaten a whole pie. Inside, he found only Mr. Spavin, Foker’s chum and half-owner of the tandem that had once dragged poor Foker into Chatteris like a runaway circus wagon. Spavin was smoking and teaching his dog tricks with a biscuit—apparently the canine was more talented than the human.

“Who’s that fellow?” Spavin thought, squinting. “Looks like he’s been dipped in fresh air and boiled in health. His hand doesn’t shake. I’d bet five to one it wouldn’t.”

Foker? Not there. Not even a trace. Mr. Spavin shrugged. “He might come back tomorrow. Or next week. Or never. Depends on whether he’s got money or not.” He asked what college Pen attended (Pendennis, naturally), offered him a pint of ale (“Very fair tap, sir”), and exchanged pleasantries until Pen politely excused himself and went to wander aimlessly along the shore.

There, beneath a sky as blue as a baby’s bonnet, he stood on the sand, biting his nails like a nervous squirrel, staring at the sea. The waves crashed in, thunderous and endless, but Pen barely noticed. Inside his head, a tidal wave of emotion was rising—faster than any ocean swell. He wanted to see Foker. Desperately. He must see Foker. Otherwise, life would be incomplete, like a play missing its final act.

“I’ll ride back,” Pen decided. “Maybe I’ll catch him on the road.”

And so, within half an hour, Rebecca was saddled again, and they were galloping down the grassy Chatteris road. About four miles from Baymouth, the Clavering turn branches off—everyone knows this, because it’s written in every local guidebook and whispered by drunken shepherds. Rebecca, being a mare with strong opinions, veered toward the fork. But Pen, with a swift cut across her shoulder, kept her going straight. They passed the turning without seeing a single red wheel or black tandem.

At the turnpike, Pen considered: “Well, if I’m this far, why not go further?” And so he rode on, eventually arriving at the George Inn.

The hostler greeted him cheerfully. “Mr. Foker’s here, sir. Been making quite a racket last night—drinking, singing, trying to fight Tom the postboy. Wouldn’t have done well, though. Tom’s a sturdy lad.”

“And did you bring up his hot water for shaving?” the hostler added, grinning at the servant emerging from the yard, carrying Foker’s clothes—brushed, pressed, and arranged with the care of a royal wardrobe master.

“Show Mr. Pendennis up,” said the hostler.

Pen followed the man to a room where, nestled in a feather bed like a sultan in a nest of pillows, lay Mr. Harry Foker.

His face was barely visible beneath the mountain of bedding. Only a little sallow chin and a red silk nightcap peeked through.

“Hullo!” said Pen.

“Who goes there?” sang out the voice from the depths. “Brother, quickly tell! What! Pendennis again? Is your mamma aware of your absence? Did you sup with us last night? No stop—who supped with us last night, Stoopid?”

“There was three officers, sir,” replied Stoopid, the domestic, with perfect seriousness. “Mr. Bingley, sir. Mr. Costigan, sir.”

“Ah yes!” Foker yawned, stretching his arms over his head like a cat. “The cup and merry jest went round. We chanted. I remember wanting to fight a postboy. Did I thrash him, Stoopid?”

“No, sir. Fight didn’t come off, sir,” said Stoopid, still solemn as a judge.

He was arranging Foker’s dressing-case—a trunk, gifted by a mother who clearly believed in extravagance. Inside: silver dishes, silver mugs, silver bottles for essences, and a full set of razors, ready for the day Foker finally grew facial hair.

“Do it some other day,” Foker muttered, yawning again. “No fight. But chanting. Bingley chanted, I chanted, the General chanted—Costigan, I mean. Did you ever hear him sing ‘The Little Pig under the Bed,’ Pen?”

“The man we met yesterday,” Pen blurted, trembling. “The father of—”

“Of the Fotheringay—the very man. Ain’t she a Venus, Pen?”

“Please, sir,” interrupted Stoopid, “Mr. Costigan’s in the sitting-room, sir. Says you asked him to breakfast. Called five times, sir. Wouldn’t wake you on no account. Has been here since eleven o’clock, sir.”

“How much is it now?”

“One, sir.”

“What would the best of mothers say,” cried Foker, rolling over dramatically, “if she saw me in bed at this hour? She sent me down here with a grinder. Wants me to cultivate my neglected genius—he, be! I say, Pen, this isn’t quite like seven o’clock school, is it, old boy?” And he laughed like a mischievous child.

“Go in and talk to the General while I dress,” he added. “And ask him to sing you ‘The Little Pig under the Bed.’ It’s capital.”

Pen, heart pounding, went to meet the General—Captain Costigan.

Now, Captain Costigan—though technically a captain, he preferred to be called “General,” perhaps because the title sounded more impressive when you were trying to collect debts—was seated in the window, reading the newspaper at arm’s length. His eyes were dim, his lips moving silently as he spelled each word. He wore a hat cocked jauntily on one ear, and one foot propped up on the windowsill, revealing boots so shabby they looked like they’d been dug out of a ditch.

Poverty, it seems, begins at the extremities. First the feet, then the hands, then the head. Captain Costigan’s extremities were suffering accordingly.

When he saw Pen, he descended from his perch with military precision—first saluting with two fingers (covered in a torn black glove), then removing his hat entirely.

He was balding, but he pulled a mass of lank iron-grey hair over his pate like a wig made of rope. Whiskers hung down on either side of his face like wet seaweed. His complexion had been ruined by whisky—once handsome, now copper-coloured, like a penny left in the rain.

He wore a high stock, stained and scarred, and a dress coat buttoned tightly, as if it were holding back something worse.

“The young gentleman to whom I had the honour to be introjuiced yesterday in the Cathedral Yard,” he announced, bowing deeply. “I hope I see you well, sir. I marked ye in the theatre last night during me daughter’s perfawrumance; and missed ye on my return. I did but conduct her home, sir, for Jack Costigan, though poor, is a gentleman; and when I reintered the house to pay me respects to me joyous young friend, Mr. Foker—ye were gone. We had a jolly night of ut, sir—Mr. Foker, the three gallant young dragoons, and your ’umble servant. Gad, sir, it put me in mind of one of our old nights when I bore His Majesty’s commission in the Foighting Hundtherd and Third.”

With great ceremony, he pulled out an ancient snuff box and presented it to Pen.

Arthur, flustered beyond belief, could barely speak. This shabby, smelly, boastful man—this was her father.

But the Captain was already regaling him with stories of past glories, pulling at his chin tuft like a dandy adjusting his cravat.

“I hope Miss F—, Miss Costigan is well, sir,” Pen stammered, blushing. “She—she gave me greater pleasure than—I—I ever enjoyed at a play. I think, sir—I think she’s the finest actress in the world.”

“Your hand, young man!” cried the Captain, seizing his hand. “For ye speak from your heart. Thank ye, sir—an old soldier and a fond father thanks ye. She is the finest actress in the world. I’ve seen the Siddons, sir, and the O’Nale—they were great, but what were they compared to Miss Fotheringay? I do not wish she should ashume her own name while on the stage. Me family, sir, are proud people; and the Costigans of Costiganstown think that an honest man, who has borne Her Majesty’s colours in the Hundred and Third, would demean himself by permitting his daughter to earn her old father’s bread.”

“There cannot be a more honourable duty, surely,” Pen said.

“Honourable! Bedad, sir, I’d like to see the man who said Jack Costigan would consent to anything dishonourable. I have a heart, sir, though I am poor; I like a man who has a heart. You have: I read it in your honest face and steady eye. And would you believe it?” he whispered, pathetically, “that that Bingley who has made his fortune by me child gives her but two guineas a week—out of which she finds herself in dresses, and which, added to me own small means, makes our all?”

Which, of course, amounted to nothing. But nobody understands how Irish lambs survive on wind and scraps.

Captain Costigan had once been dazzling: fine legs, a beautiful voice, and a pair of colours. He sang Irish ballads with such feeling that he’d cry at them himself. Brave, sentimental, generous, and utterly unreliable—he drank with anyone, backed anyone’s bill, and ended his days in a spunging-house, beloved by the sheriff’s officer who arrested him.

Before long, he extracted two sovereigns from Pen for tickets to his daughter’s benefit performance. Pen handed over his entire purse—two pounds—and feared offering more might offend the Captain’s delicate pride.

Costigan scribbled an order for a box, slipped the coins into his waistcoat, and slapped his chest like a man warming himself by a fire.

“Faith, sir,” he said, “the bullion’s scarcer with me than it used to be, as is the case with many a good fellow. I won six hundred of ’em in a single night, sir, when me kind friend, His Royal Highness the Duke of Kent, was in Gibralther.”

And so began a stream of tales about claret, bets, and races, keeping Pen amused until Foker appeared, smelling of tobacco and regret.

Breakfast arrived—devilled turkey, mutton chops—and the Captain came alive. Stories poured forth like wine. He described the death of his wife, Bessie; the challenge he sent to Captain Shanty Clancy for rudely staring at Miss Fotheringay on her carriage; the dinner at Kildare Street where six men drank twenty-one bottles of claret.

He declared it an honour to sit with such noble young gentlemen, and after a second glass of Curacoa, burst into tears.

Not a man of strong mind, certainly. But not a bad companion—just a bit of a fraud. Though he’d committed more rogueries than most honest men, he’d never lied about being poor.

After breakfast, the trio walked out. The Captain held an arm of each young man, winking at shops where he likely owed bills, as if to say, “Look who I’m with—sure I’ll pay you soon.”

They parted at a billiard room, where Foker had a date with some officers from Colonel Swallowtail’s regiment.

Pen and the Captain continued walking. The Captain, ever curious, asked about Foker’s background.

Pen told him: brewer father, aristocratic mother—Lady Agnes Milton, daughter of Lord Rosherville.

The Captain exploded into praise. “Mr. Foker’s native aristocracie!” he boomed, “could be seen with the twinkling of an oi—and only served to adawrun other qualities which he possessed, a foin intellect and a generous heart.”

Not a word of it true. But the Captain believed it all.

Pen listened, amazed, amused, confused. He didn’t doubt a thing. He took everything at face value, and Costigan, delighted by his honesty, felt compelled to invite him to his humble abode.

“Would you do me the fever,” he asked, “to enter my humble abode, where I may have the honour of inthrojuicing my dear young friend to my daughther, Miss Fotheringay?”

Pen nearly fainted. He was going to meet her! The woman whose voice had stirred his soul, whose presence had made the world spin!

He followed the Captain through Prior’s Lane, a quiet street near the cathedral, to a low gabled house with a brass plate reading “Creed, Tailor and Robe-maker.” Creed was dead. His widow opened pews in the cathedral. His son was a choir-boy with a voice like an angel—and a penchant for tossing pennies.

Two children jumped up to greet the Captain, grabbing wildly at his swallow-tails. Pen stumbled down the step, knees weak, heart hammering.

Inside, he entered a room—and froze.

A figure in black waved slightly, as if curtseying. The Captain spoke, grandly introducing Pen to “me child.”

“It is very fine weather,” Miss Fotheringay said, in a deep, melancholy Irish voice.

“Very,” Pen replied, barely able to breathe.

She sat down. Her movements were regal. Against the mantelpiece, her robe draped itself like a Greek statue. On a cane chair, her arm curved over the back, her hand poised as if awaiting a sceptre. She looked like an empress.

Her hair was blue-black, her skin dazzling white with a faint blush. Her eyes—grey, with long lashes. Her mouth—so red, so bright, so perfectly crimson, that even a geranium would envy it.

“And very warm,” she added.

“Very,” Pen agreed.

She asked about the evening. The Captain recounted the punch and the fights. Then she asked what she’d done that morning.

“Bows came,” she said, “at ten, and we studied Ophalia. It’s for the twenty-fourth, when I hope, sir, we shall have the honour of seeing ye.”

“Indeed, indeed, you will,” Pen said, wondering why she said “Ophalia” and spoke with an Irish accent, when on stage she had none.

“I’ve secured ’um for your benefit, dear,” said the Captain, tapping his waistcoat where Pen’s sovereigns lay. He winked. Pen blushed.

“Mr—the gentleman’s very obleging,” said Mrs. Haller.

“My name is Pendennis,” Pen said, blushing harder. “I—I—hope you’ll—you’ll remember it.”

His heart thumped so hard he thought he’d choke.

“Pendennis,” she said slowly, looking him straight in the eyes. Her voice was sweet, low, round. The glance—clear, bright, deadly—struck him like lightning.

“I never knew the name was so pretty before,” Pen said.

“’Tis a very pretty name,” she replied. “Pentweazle’s not a pretty name. Remember, papa, when we were on the Norwich Circuit, Young Pentweazle, who used to play second old men, and married Miss Rancy, the Columbine; they’re both engaged in London now, at the Queen’s, and get five pounds a week. Pentweazle wasn’t his real name. ’Twas Judkin gave it him, I don’t know why. His name was Harrington; that is, his real name was Potts; fawther a clergyman, very respectable. Harrington was in London, and got in debt. Ye remember; he came out in Falkland, to Mrs. Bunce’s Julia.”

“And a pretty Julia she was,” the Captain interposed. “A woman of fifty, and a mother of ten children. ’Tis you ought to have been Julia, or my name’s not Jack Costigan.”

“I didn[’t take the leading business then,” Miss Fotheringay said modestly. “I wasn’t fit for’t till Bows taught me.”

Mr. Pendennis had long since abandoned Cicero and Euripides—poor fellows!—to the dust of his study shelves. With Rebecca now firmly out of the picture (though not entirely from his thoughts), he’d found a new passion: fencing lessons in Chatteris, which were less about swordplay and more about sneaking off to see Miss Fotheringay. Honest Mr. Smirke, his tutor, was left to teach Latin verbs and trigonometry with the enthusiasm of a man who’d just discovered that no one really cared if he passed the exam.

Rebecca, poor creature, suffered most during this period. She was like a neglected horse in a stable full of fancy carriages—ignored, overlooked, and occasionally kicked by the very person who once swore eternal fidelity to her. When she went lame? Pen was as heartbroken as Richard III at Bosworth Field when his horse died under him. He didn’t just cry—he *acted*. He spent hours poring over veterinary texts, hiring doctors for her, and even considering a full-blown funeral procession. All while secretly plotting trips to Prior’s Lane.

Ah, yes—the secret trips. Once a week, under the noble pretense of studying Greek drama with Smirke, Pen would vanish into the night. He’d catch the Competitor down coach, spend two blissful hours in Chatteris (mostly at the theatre), then return on the Rival, arriving back in London just as the moon began its nightly patrol. It was a tightrope act of deception, but Pen was an expert. One time, however, disaster nearly struck. Mrs. Pendennis asked Smirke whether they’d read much the previous evening. The curate, bless him, opened his mouth to confess that he hadn’t seen Pen at all—when a boot heel came crashing down on his toe, sending a silent message: *Keep your trap shut.*

They’d talked, of course. Two men in love always do. And what a spectacle it is—watching them try to sound profound while mostly just blushing and stammering. Smirke, trembling with moral responsibility, asked Pen if his affection was “unworthy” or “unlawful.” He feared, with genuine horror, that he might have to betray his pupil to Mrs. Pendennis—and then face the wrath of both mother and son, possibly ending his career as a tutor forever.

“Unlawful?” Pen roared, half-angry, half-romantic. “She’s as pure as she is beautiful! I’d give my heart to no other woman. And if anyone dares speak ill of her, I’ll—well, dammy, I won’t stand for it!”

Smirke, chuckling nervously, said, “Well, well, don’t call me out, Arthur—I can’t fight.” But the damage was done. Now he was trapped in the web of Pen’s secret, and poor old Greek grammar suffered accordingly.

If only the reverend gentleman had glanced at the *County Chronicle* each Wednesday, he might have noticed something peculiar: poems signed “EROS,” passionate odes to “Miss Fotheringay of the Theatre Royal,” and verses so dramatic they made you wonder if someone had actually been stabbed in a garden. But since these weren’t signed “NEP” anymore—Pen’s usual pen name—neither Smirke nor Helen (who dutifully cut out all her son’s poetry from the paper) realized that Eros was none other than their own beloved Arthur.

Eventually, Mrs. Pendennis couldn’t help but notice. “Who is this lady your rival keeps singing about?” she asked one day. “He writes beautifully—but yours is better, dear Pen. Have you seen Miss Fotheringay?”

Pen nodded proudly. “Yes, Mother. Last night I saw her play Mrs. Haller in *The Stranger*. She’s going to have a benefit soon—Ophelia, Shakespeare, you know. We could go. We can get horses from the Clavering Arms.”

Laura, little Laura, jumped up with delight. “Oh, please, Mama! A play! I’ve never seen one!”

Pen mentioned “Shakespeare” because the late Mr. Pendennis had been a man of culture—sort of. He claimed there was more poetry in Shakespeare than in all of Johnson’s Poets combined, though he never actually read any of it himself. Still, he insisted Pen memorize the Bard’s works, promising one day they’d attend a real performance together. And so, when Pen suggested the play, Mrs. Pendennis teared up remembering her husband’s words. She kissed her son, said she’d go, and Laura danced around the room like a tiny whirlwind.

Helen, in a rare moment of cheer, invited Mr. Smirke along. The curate, raised in Clapham by a parent who believed theatre was the work of the devil, had never seen a play. But Shakspeare! And to sit beside Mrs. Pendennis in her carriage for an entire evening? He couldn’t resist. He gave a weak speech about temptation and gratitude, then accepted with trembling joy. As he spoke, he shot Mrs. Pendennis a look so intense she felt physically uncomfortable. It was the same look he’d given her three times already that week. Each time, she thought: *That man is becoming unbearable.*

Now, we won’t bore you with the details of Pen’s courtship—after all, you’ve already heard how charming Miss Fotheringay is. Suffice it to say, Pen poured his soul into every conversation. He told her everything: his dreams, his fears, his favorite lines from Byron, and the exact number of times he’d eaten cold mutton in the past fortnight. She listened—not because she understood, but because she looked *beautiful* doing it.

In truth, while Pen rambled on about poetry and passion, Emily Costigan (that’s her name, though everyone calls her Milly) was mentally planning how to turn her black satin dress into a bonnet like Miss Thackthwaite’s. Or perhaps how best to serve the cold mutton. She didn’t care about metaphysics. She cared about fashion, food, and not being married to some boy who thought romance was the same as riding a horse at full gallop.

But her father, Captain Costigan, was wise. On Pen’s second visit, he winked at his daughter over a glass of grog and said, “Faith, Milly darling, I think ye’ve hooked that chap.”

“Oh, papa,” she sighed, “he’s just a boy.”

Pen would have loved hearing that. He rode home wild with joy, shouting “Milly!” at the top of his lungs, as if the whole countryside should know he’d been chosen.

“I told ye,” said the Captain, “he’s not a bad fish. I asked Tom at the George—fine fortune, chariot, park, Fairoaks, only son, property at twenty-one. Ye might go further and not fare so well.”

“But boys talk,” Milly said, sipping punch. “Remember young Poldoody? He wrote me poems, but then he went abroad and got married to an Englishwoman.”

“Lord Poldoody was a nobleman,” the Captain replied. “And ye weren’t in the position ye are now. But don’t encourage him too much. Jack Costigan won’t have any thrifling with his daughter.”

“No more will his daughter,” Milly said, smirking. “A little more punch, papa? Sure, ’tis beautiful. Don’t worry about the lad—I’m old enough to take care of myself.”

So Pen came and went like a gust of wind. He rushed in, stayed for hours, then galloped away again, growing more frantic with each visit. Sometimes the Captain was there; sometimes not. When he was, he’d cock his hat and strut off like a peacock, leaving the young couple alone. Their meetings took place in the drawing-room—a low-ceilinged, wainscoted chamber with a large window overlooking the Dean’s garden. Sunlight streamed in, illuminating Emily’s lovely face as she sat quietly at her embroidery, looking calm and serene, while Pen ranted about life, love, and the meaning of existence.

Occasionally, the great cathedral bell would boom, and Pen would pause, smiling, until the sound faded. Or the rooks would squawk in the elms. Or the organ music would drift across the air, softening even the most impassioned speech.

By the way, Miss Fotheringay attended church every Sunday without fail. In a plain shawl and close bonnet, she sat beside her father, who responded to prayers in a voice so rich and brogue-heavy it sounded like a hymn sung by a dragon. He joined in the psalms, chanted with fervor, and behaved like a model Christian—though he probably did it more for show than anything else.

Little Bows, the family’s house-friend and a cripple who couldn’t appear on stage (being even more deformed than Bingley, the manager), was furious at the idea of Emily marrying a mere country squire. He’d taught her to act—shrieking lines in his cracked voice, twisting her arms into perfect poses, drilling her until she could perform the same gestures, tones, and tears with mechanical precision. She wasn’t clever. She wasn’t passionate. She was simply obedient. But she was magnificent on stage—like a clockwork doll powered by emotion.

Bows believed she deserved better. A London engagement! A great success! But the London managers had already seen her—and she’d failed miserably three years prior. That was why Bows had taken her in hand. And that was why, despite her lack of intelligence, Pen fell madly in love with her.
