The day of the play arrived. The post-horses from the Clavering Arms carried the party to Chatteris. Pen was thrilled to see a decent-sized crowd gathered. In the front box sat Mr. Foker and Mr. Spavin, resplendent in full evening attire. They greeted Pen warmly and admired his mother—Mrs. Pendennis, dressed in black velvet and wearing her diamond cross, looked regal. Little Laura, red-cheeked and full of curls, was adorable. Behind them sat Pen and Smirke, the latter blushing furiously at being in such a place—yet utterly delighted to be there.

Samuel, the coachman, took his seat in the pit, where Mr. Foker’s man also stood guard. The orchestra included non-commissioned officers of the Dragoons, led by Colonel Swallowtail himself—portly, proud, and wearing his Waterloo medal like a crown.

“Who’s that odd-looking fellow bowing to you, Arthur?” Mrs. Pendennis asked.

Pen flushed. “Captain Costigan, ma’am. A Peninsular officer.” Which was true—except that Captain Costigan was also Emily’s father, and currently waving a white kid glove dramatically at Pen.

No one knew this. Not yet.

Mr. Hornbull played Hamlet. Mr. Bingley, modestly, took Horatio. Then came *Black-Eyed Susan*, which everyone adored.

But the real star was Ophelia. Emily Costigan—now Miss Fotheringay—was breathtaking. She laughed, wept, danced wildly, flung flowers, and sang snatches of songs with such charm that even the most stoic spectator melted. Her hair tossed freely, her eyes sparkled, and when she lay dead in the grave, she looked so perfectly tragic that Pen almost wanted to join her.

As Hamlet and Laertes fought above her, she peeked from behind the scenes, glancing toward Pen’s box with a hint of recognition. And there, twinkling in her breast, was a locket—bought by Pen from Mr. Nathan in High Street, with his last shilling and a sovereign borrowed from Smirke.

When the curtain fell, the house erupted. Smirke clapped wildly, shouting “Bravo!” as if he’d written the play himself. The Dragoons rose to their feet. Colonel Swallowtail waved his cap. Even Mrs. Pendennis waved her handkerchief. Little Laura danced, laughed, and stared up at Pen in awe.

Pen bellowed louder than anyone: “Fotheringay! Fotheringay!” He leaned over his mother’s chair, waving his hat, barely containing himself. He wanted to scream: *That’s her! That’s the woman I love!* But he held his tongue—just barely.

After the show, they returned home in the carriage. Pen sat on the box with Smirke, smoking a cigar under the moonlight. Mr. Foker’s tandem whizzed past, and Mr. Spavin serenaded them with variations of *Rule Britannia* on his key-bugle.

Two days later, the Dean of Chatteris hosted a dinner for clerical friends. Port wine flowed. The Bishop was insulted. Doctor Portman, gallant and observant, stepped out onto the lawn and offered Mrs. Dean his arm. They walked slowly across the ancient grass, bathed in golden twilight.

Meanwhile, at the far end of the garden, in Mrs. Creed’s house, another scene unfolded. Pen and Emily were alone in the first-floor room. The windows were open. The air was warm. And Pen—poor fool—had finally spoken.

Trembling, breathless, eyes wide with emotion, he confessed everything. His love. His admiration. His soul. He threw himself at her feet, a modern-day knight offering his heart to a queen.

“Does your mother know?” she asked, slowly.

He seized her hand and kissed it a thousand times. She didn’t pull away.

“She likes you,” he whispered. “She admires you. She’ll love you. Who wouldn’t?”

“’Deed then,” she said, perhaps with pity, “I think you do.”

Then he launched into a rhapsody—love, eternity, truth, and all the rest. We won’t repeat it. Let him have his moment. Let the foolish boy pour out his heart. For what good is wisdom if you can’t feel?

At the climax, he kissed her hand again—just as Master Ridley Roset, her son, tugged his mother’s dress and pointed upward.

“I say, ma! Look up there!”

And there they were—Pen, kneeling, kissing the fingers of the woman who could neither love nor understand him, while the Dean and Doctor Portman watched in stunned silence.

Dr. Portman froze. His prime pupil—his favorite—was making a spectacle of himself. And worse—this was no ordinary girl. This was *her*. The daughter of Captain Costigan. The actress. The object of a hundred rumors.

He stood mute. Rage and disbelief warred within him.

And so ended the chapter—not with a bang, but with a quiet, awkward stare from a child, a frozen scholar, and a boy whose heart had leapt too far, too fast, into the hands of a woman who’d rather be thinking about her next bonnet.

CHAPTER VII. In Which the Major Makes His Grand Entrance (With a Side of Snoring, Rum, and Regret)

Major Arthur Pendennis arrived at Fairoaks like a storm cloud after a particularly grueling night—though, to be fair, it wasn’t the weather that had him looking like he’d been dragged through a hedge backwards by a bad-tempered donkey. No, it was the mail-coach. That infernal, rattling, jolting tin can of a vehicle that once upon a time passed for travel in England.

The journey had been a symphony of discomfort: a man with the build of a barrel and the social grace of a startled badger had squashed him into the corner like a piece of unwanted luggage; said gentleman then proceeded to snore like a foghorn on full blast, punctuated by occasional grunts that suggested he might be dreaming of wrestling bears or perhaps just digesting a very large meal. Opposite sat a widow lady whose idea of fresh air seemed to involve sealing all windows shut and then dousing the cabin in the fumes of Jamaica rum-and-water, which she sipped from a bottle concealed in her reticule like a secret agent delivering poison. Every sip sent waves of aromatic despair through the coach.

And when the Major managed to steal a moment’s sleep? Ah! Then came the horn at the turnpike gate—blaring like a trumpet announcing the arrival of doom—or the neighbour wedging himself even tighter, or the widow’s feet, which seemed to have a personal vendetta against his toes. Poor Major Pendennis emerged at Fairoaks looking less like a nobleman and more like a man who’d survived a siege and lost the battle for dignity.

But such were the joys of the jolly old days! Eight miles an hour for twenty hours, a hard seat, gouty limbs, grumbling coachmen, and fellow-passengers who believed spirits-and-water were essential to good health—why, one could only marvel at how anyone endured it, let alone enjoyed it. And yet they did. They laughed. They ate pie. They complained. And somehow, they made it home.

Among the passengers were two schoolboys returning for midsummer holidays, their enthusiasm as bright as their cravats. The Major watched in mild astonishment as they devoured a feast at Bagshot Inn—ham, eggs, pickles, boiled beef, tea, coffee—you name it, they loaded it into their saddlebags like modern-day gluttons with no sense of moderation. Meanwhile, the Major sipped a cup of tea so weak it might have been brewed from yesterday’s dreams.

But the boys’ cheerfulness struck a chord. One of them, it turned out, was the son of a lord—someone the Major had met in London’s glittering circles. The boy slept through the entire ordeal, undisturbed by the chaos around him, and woke up as fresh as a daisy (and proudly declared as much), while the Major looked like he’d been dipped in mud and left out in the rain.

At last, the Major reached Fairoaks. He descended at the lodge gate, yellow-faced, wig askew, beard bristling like a porcupine, and every joint screaming in protest. The porteress and gardener’s wife greeted him with reverence. Mr. Morgan, his manservant, received even greater deference—because, as we shall see, Morgan was not merely a valet but a master of mystery.

Helen, ever watchful, saw the Major arrive from her window. But she didn’t rush to meet him. She knew better. The Major disliked surprises. He required preparation—like a king before a coronation, or a man before facing a room full of relatives who might ask awkward questions about his wardrobe choices.

Ah yes—the famous morocco box incident. When Pen was a boy, he had committed the unforgivable sin of stealing the Major’s false teeth from the dressing table. A crime so grave it nearly caused a family rift. The Major would never appear without his teeth, nor without his wig—Morgan guarded both like sacred relics, curling the wig in secret corners, introducing it to the Master’s room with the solemnity of a priest bringing forth holy water.

So the Major retreated to his chamber, groaning, moaning, wheezing, and cursing Morgan under his breath—much like an old bear who’s just been woken from hibernation by a sudden cold snap. After what felt like an eternity of grooming, he finally emerged—belted, curled, and properly dignified—descending into the drawing-room with the air of a man who had seen things, done things, and still expected everyone to bow.

But Pen was nowhere to be found. Only Helen and little Laura, sewing quietly at her knees. The Major offered her a single forefinger—a gesture of greeting so formal it might have been a diplomatic protocol. Laura took it trembling, dropped it, and fled from the room.

The Major didn’t care. In fact, he preferred it. Laura was not his favourite personage. He had private reasons for disliking her—one day, perhaps, we’ll reveal them. For now, let us say she disappeared into the house, searching for Pen.

She found him in the orchard, pacing back and forth in deep conversation with Mr. Smirke, the tutor. So absorbed was Pen that he didn’t hear Laura’s voice until Smirke tugged his coat and pointed toward her. She ran up, hand outstretched.

“Come in, Pen,” she said. “There’s somebody come—Uncle Arthur’s here.”

“He is, is he?” Pen replied, gripping her hand tightly. He turned to Smirke with an expression that said, *I’m ready. Bring it on.* Mr. Smirke sighed gently, raised his eyes to heaven, and muttered something about the perils of youth.

“Lead on, Laura,” Pen said, half-fierce, half-laughing. “Lead on, and say I wait upon my uncle.”

But beneath the bravado, he was terrified. These past two days, he’d confided everything to Mr. Smirke—his passion for Miss Emily Fotheringay, his belief in love, his determination to marry her despite all obstacles. And Mr. Smirke, himself hopelessly in love with the idea of love, had listened with rapt attention, nodding sagely, occasionally muttering, “Yes, yes, quite right,” though he’d never actually seen the woman in question.

Pen had spoken of Emily as if she were a goddess—pure, refined, brilliant, kind-hearted, and utterly deserving of a life beyond the stage. He described her wit, her grace, her genius, her devotion to her father—a nobleman fallen on hard times but still respected in high society. He insisted he wouldn’t marry until he was twenty-one, but also swore—face solemn as a funeral—that this was the one true passion of his life, and death itself would be preferable to failure.

Helen, naturally, had tried to reason with him. She’d reminded him of long engagements, of tragic outcomes, of her own cousin—Laura’s father—who had married above his station and died young, broken-hearted. But Pen dismissed her fears with a mix of fiery logic and poetic flair, convincing her almost completely that if God had ordained this match, then it was meant to be.

And so, during those two days, Pen had practically won over his mother—though not without effort. He’d written verses late into the night, composing odes to Emily so grand and ridiculous that even the most romantic soul might have blinked. One night, Helen passed by his door and heard him mumbling poetry in bed. She peeked in—only to find him biting a pencil, papers scattered everywhere, his face flushed with passion.

“Arthur,” she whispered.

He jumped, shoved papers under the pillow, and mumbled, “I couldn’t sleep. I—I was writing.”

Then, suddenly, he flung his arms around her neck. “O mother! I love her, I love her!”

How could any mother resist? She held him close, remembering when he was a child, when she used to pray over his bed. Now he was a man—madly, dangerously in love.

When the Major finally appeared, dressed and presentable, he found Helen already distressed—not just about the marriage, but about the possibility that Pen might suffer terribly if it were denied. She begged him to be gentle. “He has a high spirit,” she warned. “Dr. Portman spoke harshly to him the other night—and it frightened me. Please, be careful.”

The Major took her hand, kissed it gallantly, and smiled with the air of a man who knew exactly what was going on. “Bon Dieu!” he thought. “The boy has talked the woman round. She’d give him a wife like a toy if he cried for it. Where are the lettres-de-cachet when you need them?”

He pressed her hand, placed it gently on the table, and looked her straight in the eye. “Confess,” he said, “you’re wondering how you can possibly justify letting him have his way.”

She blushed. “I’m thinking he’s unhappy—and I am too.”

“To contradict him or to let him have his wish?” he asked.

“I’m thinking… that he’s formed such a foolish, cruel, fatal attachment,” she said, “one that will end in pain, whatever happens.”

“No,” said the Major firmly. “We are not going to have a Pendennis marry a strolling mountebank from a booth. No, no—we won’t marry into Greenwich Fair, ma’am.”

“If the match is broken suddenly,” Helen interposed, “I don’t know what may happen. Arthur’s temper is ardent. His affections are intense. I tremble at the thought.”

“My dear madam,” said the Major, with deep sympathy, “I have no doubt Arthur will suffer terribly. But is he the only one who has suffered such a disappointment?”

“No,” Helen whispered, her mind drifting back to her own youth.

“And I,” said the Major, lowering his voice, “have known such sorrow myself. A young woman—fifteen thousand pounds, niece to an Earl—most accomplished. Half her money would’ve promoted me to lieutenant-colonel by thirty. But it wasn’t to be. I was penniless. Her parents interfered. I went to India. I served Lord Buckley. Without her. We exchanged letters. We returned our hair. We suffered—but we recovered. She’s now a baronet’s wife with thirteen children. Altered, yes—but her daughters remind me of what she was. The third is to be presented next week.”

Helen said nothing. She was seventeen again.

“Look at my brother,” the Major continued. “He too had a disappointment. Miss Balls—daughter of a doctor in large practice. Nearly succeeded. But difficulties arose. And look—he ended up with *you*.”

He gently squeezed her fingers.

“Marriages between people of different rank,” Helen said softly, “are often sad. Laura’s father—my cousin—was brought up with me. He married above his station. It ended badly.”

“Most injudicious,” the Major cut in. “Imagine marrying a woman of low birth, and having your house filled with her vulgar relations! Imagine your wife clinging to a mother who drops her h’s or calls Maria Marire! How do you introduce her into society? I’ve seen men ruined—cut, lost—by their wives’ connections. Lady Snapperton refused to receive Lady Brouncker because she was a druggist’s daughter. Tom Wagg said she never needed medicine—she never had an ‘h’ in her life. Good Ged, what would a brief separation be compared to a lifetime of misalliance?”

“What, indeed,” Helen murmured, half-laughing, half-sighing.

“And this woman is ten years older than Arthur. What happens in such cases? Misery. Undeviating misery. Look at Lord Clodworthy—his wife looks like his mother. Lord Willowbank—twice he’s cut her down when she’s hanged herself out of jealousy. Mark my words—next time, he won’t cut her down. No, my dear madam, you’re not in the world. I am. You’re sentimental. You must leave this to my experience. Marry this woman? Marry an actress at eighteen? Bah bah! I’d sooner send him to the kitchen and marry the cook.”

“I know the evils of premature engagements,” Helen sighed—for the third time in the conversation.

And so, with these thoughts swirling, the chapter ends—just as we begin to wonder: who is little Laura? Why does the Major dislike her? And what secrets lie behind her quiet presence in the house?

Perhaps the answer lies in the next chapter.

CHAPTER VIII. In which Pen is kept waiting at the Door, while the Reader is informed who little Laura was.

Once upon a time—yes, once upon a time—there lived a young gentleman of Cambridge University named Reverend Francis Bell, M.A., Fellow and Tutor, whose life was so full of learning, virtue, and romantic entanglements that it might have made a novel by itself—if only he’d had the sense to keep a diary instead of letting his heart do all the writing.

This gentleman, Mr. Bell, came down from the hallowed halls of academia to spend the long vacation in a quiet village where Miss Helen Thistlewood resided with her mother—the widow of a gallant lieutenant slain at Copenhagen. As cousin to the lady (and nephew to Mrs. Thistlewood), Francis Bell was naturally invited to lodge under her roof, which was modest but charmingly furnished with books, memories, and the faint scent of lavender.

He taught three or four pupils during the summer months, reading them Latin, Greek, and moral philosophy with such gravity that even the children began to suspect he was secretly preparing for sainthood. But beneath this austere exterior beat a heart once wildly given over to love—a love as pure, as passionate, and as ill-fated as any tale ever spun by a moonstruck poet.

You see, young Francis had fallen headlong into affection at seventeen, when he was still too awkward to walk without tripping over his own feet and too impulsive to consider consequences. It happened in the garden of Mr. Coacher, a parson who had tutored him in his youth. There, amid the peas and the peonies, Francis found himself on his knees before Miss Martha Coacher, helping her gather beans and offering her an eternal vow of devotion.

Miss Martha was not exactly a spring blossom; she was several years older than he, and had been wounded by love more times than one could count. The apothecary had jilted her. The dragoon officer had galloped off into the sunset with no intention of returning. Her heart, like a well-worn book, had been dog-eared by disappointment.

But here was Francis—earnest, gawky, sincere—and though she laughed at his youthful earnestness (“Law, Bell, I’m sure you are too young to think of such things!”), she did not refuse his offer. She simply tucked away the idea in her virgin bosom, like a secret letter meant for future reading.

And then—oh, the drama!—she sent him a lock of her hair, tied up in blue ribbon, as proof of her acceptance. Not that she hadn’t done this before. Three previous lovers had received similar tokens—only to vanish like smoke. But Francis was different. He was faithful. He studied hard. He won prizes. He became a Wrangler. He earned a fellowship. And through it all, he wrote letters to Martha that were tender, respectful, and occasionally so poetic they must have made her weep into her teacup.

But time passed. Francis turned twenty-six. Miss Martha turned thirty-four. The glow of their early romance dimmed. The pea-garden sunshine faded. And poor Francis realized, with horror, that he was engaged to a woman who was coarse, ill-tempered, ill-favoured, and frankly quite dreadful.

Yet he couldn’t break it. Not yet. His word was pledged—thousands of times, in letters that now read like confessions of a man slowly losing his mind. And so, instead of going to Bearleader’s Green (Mr. Coacher’s parish), he took refuge in his aunt’s village, where he met Helen Thistlewood—now grown into a beautiful young woman.

They walked together. They read aloud. She sang for him. He taught her everything. And soon, both knew what the other felt. He had shown her the portrait of his fiancée, blushing as he did so. He had let her read the cruel, demanding letters from the woman he was bound to marry. And still, she loved him.

It was a tender, tragic affair. But duty called. And so, when October arrived, Francis returned to college, leaving behind a broken-hearted girl and a shattered dream.

Back at Cambridge, he wrestled with his conscience. Should he confess? Should he give up his savings and beg for release? He hesitated. Time dragged on. Then came the discovery: Martha had learned of his new attachment.

She threatened him with public shame—“I will carry your letters into every court in the kingdom!”—and then, with theatrical finality, declared she would kill herself if he broke his promise.

So Francis did what any man of honour would do: he married the woman. He became vicar of a small living. He tried to be happy. But he was already old, worn out, and spiritually dead.

Years later, in Coventry Island, far from England, he fathered a daughter—Helen Laura. Her mother died shortly after childbirth, having whispered, “I was old, was I? I was inferior, was I? But I married you, Mr. Bell, and kept you from marrying her.” And then she expired.

Francis remarried—a colonial lady, kinder, gentler—but fate mocked him again. She died in childbirth. And so, alone and broken, he sent his daughter back to England, to be raised by Helen Pendennis and her husband, with a parting prayer that they would care for her.

The child arrived from Bristol, dressed in black, weeping bitterly as her nurse departed. But under Helen’s loving care, she dried her tears. Around her neck hung a locket—containing a lock of hair from the woman who had once loved Francis Bell and lost him forever.

Her name was Helen Laura. But John Pendennis, her adoptive father, insisted she be called Laura—not Helen. He disapproved of her origin, her mother’s past, and her very existence. She was afraid of him until the day he died.

Only then did Helen dare to show her true affection for the girl. And thus, Laura Bell became Mrs. Pendennis’s daughter—though neither her husband nor the Major liked her much. She wasn’t noble. She didn’t come from a great family. She had only two thousand pounds. And worst of all—she reminded them of painful truths better left buried.

Now, at last, let us return to our hero—Arthur Pendennis—who has been standing outside the door, bracing himself for battle.

He had rehearsed the scene in his mind: dramatic entrance, stern face, noble bearing. He would confront the dreaded uncle, stand tall, and declare his love with the dignity of a Pendennis.

With a flourish, he flung open the door and marched in—lance couched, plumes flying, ready for war.

The Major, however, was not impressed. He merely grinned at the boy’s ridiculous pomposity. He had anticipated this moment. He had seen it coming.

Instead of thundering threats, he smiled, held out his hands, and said cheerfully, “Well, Pen, my boy, tell us all about it.”

Helen was delighted. Pen was devastated. His grand entrance had been reduced to absurdity. He blushed. He stammered. “I—I didn’t know you were here,” he mumbled. “Is—is town very full?”

The Major nearly burst out laughing. So did Mrs. Pendennis. And so, feeling helpless, she kissed him.

Pen, touched by her tenderness, almost cried.

“What a couple of fools,” thought the Major. “If I hadn’t come down, she’d have driven over in state to bless the young lady’s family.”

“Come, come,” he said, still grinning, “let’s have as little sentiment as possible. Now, Pen, tell us the whole story.”

Pen regained his composure. “The story is,” he said, “as I’ve written to you before. I’ve met a most beautiful and virtuous lady—of high birth, though in reduced circumstances. I’ve found the woman in whom I know my happiness lies. I cannot think of any other. I am aware of the age difference, and other difficulties—but my affection is so strong I believe we can overcome them. She has consented to marry me. I’ve promised her my heart and my fortune.”

“How much is that?” asked the Major. “Has anyone left you money? I don’t know that you’re worth a shilling.”

“You know what I have is hers,” cried Mrs. Pendennis.

“Good heavens, madam, hold your tongue!” the Major thought—but wisely refrained.

“No doubt,” he said calmly, “you would sacrifice anything for him. But really, Pen, it’s *your* fortune you’re offering. And you’re eighteen. You haven’t consulted your mother, have you?”

“I know my mother will give me anything,” Pen said, looking uneasy.

“Yes, but reason exists,” replied the Major. “When you hand over the house and transfer the bank account to Miss Costigan—don’t you think you should ask your sister-in-law’s opinion? I speak as a man of the world to another. Would it not have been more generous to wait? To ask her leave?”

Pen lowered his head. He began to see that his romantic gesture—so noble in his imagination—was actually a selfish, reckless act.

“I did it in a moment of passion,” he confessed. “I didn’t know what I was doing. But now it’s said. I stand by it. I’ll die rather than take it back. And I don’t want to burden my mother. I’ll work for myself. I’ll go on the stage. She says I’d do well there.”

“But will she take you on those terms?” the Major interjected. “I don’t say Miss Costigan isn’t noble. But don’t you suppose your position—your name, your prospects—makes a difference in how she views your attentions?”

“I’ll die rather than break my word,” Pen repeated, clenching his fists.

“Who asks you to?” said the Major. “No gentleman breaks his word. But you can wait. You owe something to your mother. Something to your family. Something to me, as your father’s representative.”

“Oh, of course,” Pen said, relieved.

“Well, then,” said the Major, “give us another promise. That you won’t run off to Scotland. No clandestine marriages.”

“That would be a falsehood,” said Helen. “Pen never lies to his mother.”

Pen’s eyes filled with shame. Hadn’t he lied to her? All along?

He gave his uncle his hand. “No, sir,” he said, “on my word of honour, I will never marry without my mother’s consent.”

And with a bright look at Helen, he left the room.

“He’s an angel,” exclaimed Mrs. Pendennis.

“He comes of good stock,” said the Major, kissing her glove. “On both sides.”

He was pleased with himself. He had handled it with finesse. And so, after dinner, he continued his gentle diplomacy over wine.

“You must bear in mind,” he said, “that you’re throwing yourself away. Your mother may submit. But she won’t be happy. You’re choosing a woman from a country theatre over one of the finest ladies in England. And I’ve often thought—between ourselves—that my sister might have wanted to marry you to Flora, Laura—what’s-her-name? I always intended to prevent it. The child has only two thousand pounds. My sister struggles to maintain her household. I had higher hopes for you. With your name, your talents, and my connections, I could have placed you in a remarkable position. A general? A diplomat? A statesman? But now—marrying a woman of thirty from a barn? Public service is closed. Society is closed. The bar is your only hope—and even then, you’ll be marrying out of a kitchen. Or you may come live here—down here, mon Dieu!—for the rest of your life. Where your mother will welcome your wife with kindness. Where no one will visit you. Where I, for one, shall be shy of visiting you myself. Because I like to live with gentlemen. And you’ll end up drinking rum-and-water with farmers and drunkards. It’s no affair of mine. I’m not angry. But I pity you. And I pity your mother.”

He paused. “Pass the claret. It’s some I sent to your father. I bought it at Lord Levant’s sale.”

Then, with a smug nod, he added: “Of course, having made your promise, you’ll do what’s right. But promise us this: no secrets. No trips. Keep studying. Write to her—how often?”

Pen blushed. “Why… yes. I’ve written.”

And so the conversation ended—not with triumph, but with a quiet understanding that love, like history, is often written in blood, tears, and regret.

CHAPTER IX. In Which the Major Launches His Campaign Against Love, Romance, and Common Sense

Let us pause for a moment to honor the true hero of this tale—Major Pendennis, a man whose devotion to duty, family, and social propriety knew no bounds, except perhaps the bounds of his own sanity. For what greater sacrifice could a gentleman make than to abandon London in May? Not just the newspapers and morning walks (though those were treasures), but the entire glittering apparatus of high society: the club-hopping afternoons, the whispered confidences with ladies of title, the jaunty rides through Rotten Row, the opera stalls where he sat like a king, and even the Saturday excursions to Fulham or Richmond—where one might see a nobleman’s hat bobbing over a hedge, or catch a glimpse of a duke’s carriage passing by.

And yet—this was not all. He gave up the very *idea* of London. The Morning Post no longer bore his name in its social pages; the Court Guide lost its charm. He resigned himself to a quiet country house, surrounded by a widow (his sister-in-law), a boy who had never seen a proper dinner party, a curate who spoke of God as if he were a distant cousin, and a ten-year-old girl who probably thought her uncle was a bit odd.

But here is the rub: the Major didn’t do it for virtue. He did it for *duty*. And because he believed, with the solemnity of a man who’d once been at the head of a regiment, that such sacrifices were necessary for the good of the nation—and especially for the salvation of young Pen.

Now, you may think this sounds noble. You may even weep at the image of him refusing invitations with a sigh, as though each refusal were a martyrdom. But let us be honest: few people noticed. Helen, Pen’s mother, merely smiled when he lamented the loss of “the Court Guide.” She didn’t understand the weight of his sorrow—how could she? She wasn’t a man of the world. But young Pen? Ah, Pen listened. He listened with wide eyes, like a child who has just discovered that there are whole continents beyond his village.

The Major, ever the mentor, filled his nephew’s ears with tales of royalty, dukes, and fashionable scandals. He regaled him with stories of Admiral Bowser, who had “rat-ted” to Ministers (a serious offense, according to the Major), only to have his reputation shredded by a young man named Mr. Suckling—whose joke about the Admiral’s conduct was interrupted by none other than Lady Grampound Bowser’s daughter, sitting right beside him, and Lord Grampound Bowser’s son-in-law across the table.

“Imagine,” said the Major, “the horror! The man was doomed. Never again will he dine in South Street. I promise you.”

Pen absorbed these lessons like a sponge. He learned that genealogy was more important than theology, and that reading Debrett every day was essential. “Not so much the history,” the Major explained, “as the alliances. A man’s career can be ruined by ignorance on this point.” He then paused, as if waiting for Pen to nod wisely. Pen nodded.

He also advised Pen on church attendance. “It don’t matter so much in town,” the Major said, “but when a gentleman is sur ses terres, he must set an example.” He mentioned the Duke of Saint David’s, who sang in church—“a doosed fine effect from the family pew”—and added that Pen, being the first man in the parish (since the Claverings were away), should consider himself “as good as any.”

“Not if you marry a lady whom the country folk won’t meet,” he warned. “That would be unfortunate.”

Ah, yes—the marriage question. This was the Major’s favorite topic. He returned to it constantly, like a clockwork bird returning to its perch. Pen, poor fellow, began to feel that every conversation was leading to the same conclusion: *You’re throwing yourself away.*

And why not? It’s easy to convince a boy that he’s destined for greatness. Pen, eager for approval, took his uncle’s advice seriously. He found Captain Costigan—Emily’s father—increasingly unbearable. The man reeked of punch, wore clothes that looked as if they’d been dug out of a cellar, and had a habit of staring at the ceiling during conversations as if trying to remember where he left his dignity.

Pen couldn’t bring this man near his mother. He couldn’t imagine introducing him to anyone. Even thinking about it made him shudder. And yet—here was the cruel irony—he had already written to Costigan, enclosing a sovereign and signing off as “your affectionate son, Arthur Pendennis.”

Yes. That’s right. He’d called himself someone else’s son.

When the Major questioned him about Emily, Pen faltered. Was she accomplished? No. Clever? Well, average. Could he show some letters? Only three—invitations and replies, nothing passionate, nothing poetic. “She is cautious enough,” the Major said, dry as a bone. “Older than you, my poor boy.”

Then came the apology: a humble, heartfelt plea from a fond uncle who only wanted to protect the family’s honor. But Pen was furious. He swore, before Emily herself, before Miss Rouncy (the confidante), before the Captain himself, that he would never betray Emily. The Captain, moved to tears, embraced him, wiped his eye with a hand that smelled faintly of gin, and declared that no man should meddle with his daughter’s heart.

Pen shuddered. He thought of his uncle. He thought of the life he was supposed to lead.

And so he avoided Chatteris. Not because he didn’t love Emily—but because he feared his uncle’s judgment. Every time he rode over, the Major seemed to know exactly why. There was no hiding it. One day, Pen walked half a mile beyond the Lodge gates to wait for the Competitor coach. On the roof of the Fairoaks Inn, a man waved. It was Mr. Morgan—the Major’s valet. Who else?

Mr. Morgan had been sent to investigate. He’d gone to Chatteris multiple times. He’d questioned waiters, ostlers, barflies. He learned that Captain Costigan was broke, drunk, and banned from the messroom. He’d asked the actors at the theatre—“What do you think of Miss Fotheringay?”—and received answers like, “She’s a splendid woman. Very careful. Her father watches her like a hawk.”

Mrs. Creed, the pew-opener, confirmed it. Emily never entertained alone. Always a chaperone—a child, a teacher, a theater girl. Once, when Pen visited, she’d come downstairs only after ensuring Mrs. Creed was present.

So the Major sighed. “If it were but a temporary liaison,” he said, “one could bear it. But a virtuous attachment? That’s the deuce. It comes from being brought up by women.”

Doctor Portman disagreed. “Nothing could be better for Pen than a virtuous attachment,” he said. “But this one—I deplore it.”

“And I say,” replied the Major, “that the best way to stop him from marrying her is to make him marry *someone else* tomorrow.”

They discussed it. Then Doctor Portman suggested something bold: “Suppose we go and see her?”

The Major laughed. “I hadn’t thought of that. After all, she’s not my niece. She’s Miss Fotheringay the actress. We’ve as good a right as any other man to see her—if we pay our money.”

So they went. They dined at the George Inn, where two other guests sat quietly—officer and student, both unremarkable. Then they hurried to the theatre. It was *Hamlet*, of course. Shakespeare was Article XL of Doctor Portman’s creed. He always saw it once a year, just to prove he was still a man of culture.

Miss Fotheringay played Ophelia. As usual, she delivered the same performance night after night—precise, rehearsed, flawless. But the Major and the Doctor watched with new eyes. They saw the beauty, the grace, the intelligence. “Gad,” muttered the Major, “the young rascal hasn’t made a bad choice.”

The Doctor clapped loudly. “She’s a clever actress. And remarkably attractive.”

And then the Major pointed to the stage-box. “Look,” he said. “That young dragoon is clearly smitten.”

Indeed, the young officer was applauding wildly. And Miss Fotheringay—well, she glanced at him with a look that could only be described as… encouraging.

The Major closed his opera-glass with a snap. “No more tonight,” he said.

They left. The Doctor returned to his wife at the Deanery. The Major walked back to the George, where he had reserved a bed.

And as he passed under the gaslight, he thought not of romance, nor of duty, nor even of Pen.

He thought of the next move.

Because in the game of love, as in war, the best strategy is often to watch your enemy closely—and then strike when they least expect it.

Major Pendennis sauntered home like a man who’d just survived a minor war—possibly against his own conscience, possibly against the weather, but definitely not against any enemy of note. He arrived at the George Inn to find Mr. Morgan, his valet, standing like a sentry outside the door, as if guarding the entrance to some sacred chamber of domesticity.

“Sir,” said Morgan, with all the solemnity of a man announcing a royal visit, “there’s a young gentleman in the coffee-room you might care to see.”

“Is Arthur there?” asked the Major, already bracing himself for a lecture on duty, honor, and the proper way to wear a cravat.

“No, sir,” replied Morgan, “but his great friend, Mr. Foker, is. Lady Agnes Foker’s son. He’s been asleep since dinner, sir—just rang for coffee—and I thought, perhaps, you might fancy a chat.”

With that, he opened the door like a stagehand revealing a scene from *The Comedy of Errors*.

Inside sat Mr. Henry Foker, looking every inch the idle aristocrat who’d just woken up from a nap induced by too much food and too little ambition. His eyes were still half-closed, his legs sprawled across the bench, and the table before him bore the wreckage of a feast: empty decanters, crumbs, and what looked suspiciously like a forgotten slice of plum pudding. The play had clearly been cancelled in favor of a more important performance: the art of dozing off mid-conversation.

The Major hesitated—how does one approach someone who has just emerged from a coma of gastronomic excess? But Foker, ever the social virtuoso, beat him to it.

“Like to look at the evening paper, sir?” he asked, lifting the *Globe* as though offering a peace treaty.

“Thank you,” said the Major, accepting it with a bow that suggested he was receiving an order of chivalry.

“I believe I’m speaking to Mr. Henry Foker,” he continued, “Lady Agnes’s son. I’ve had the pleasure of knowing her ladyship—though I must say, you’ve got quite the Rosherville face about you.”

Foker blinked. “Hullo! I beg your pardon—I took you for a commercial gent.” Then he stopped, realizing how rude that sounded. “To whom have I the pleasure of speaking?”

“To a relative of a friend and schoolfellow of yours—Arthur Pendennis, my nephew. He’s spoken highly of you, and I’m Major Pendennis, whom he may have mentioned.”

“Sir, you do me proud!” said Foker, beaming. “And so you’re Arthur’s uncle?”

“And guardian,” added the Major, puffing out his chest slightly.

“He’s as good a fellow as ever stepped,” said Foker. “Clever too—I was always a bit of a dunce myself, but I know talent when I see it.”

“You show both taste and modesty,” said the Major, flattered.

“Ah, well,” said Foker, wagging his head, “I’m no bookworm. Never could manage Latin or Greek. But Pendennis—he used to write half the class’s poetry. Still—” he paused dramatically—“you’re his guardian, aren’t you? And I hope you won’t mind me saying this: I think he’s what we call… flat.”

The Major nearly dropped the *Globe*. “Flat?”

“About women,” explained Foker, winking as though he were sharing a state secret. “You know—romantic affairs. He’s gone and fallen for Miss Fotheringay—Costigan’s real name, you know—an absolute stunner. I introduced him to the General—her father—a regular old rogue, loves his whisky-and-water—but now they’re thick as thieves. And I’m blessed if he hasn’t proposed.”

He slapped the table so hard the dessert trembled.

“What!” said the Major. “You know about it?”

“Know it? Of course I do. We were talking about it at mess yesterday—chaffing poor Sir Derby Oaks until he was fit to burst. Know him? Dined together. We were smoking outside the theatre when you passed in for dinner.”

“I remember Sir Thomas Oaks,” said the Major, “before he became a baronet. Lived in Cavendish Square, physician to Queen Charlotte.”

“The young one,” said Foker, “is making the money spin. And yes—he’s another suitor.”

“Another what?”

“Another admirer of Miss Fotheringay. We call him Mondays, Wednesdays, and Fridays. Pen’s Tuesdays, Thursdays, and Saturdays. But don’t worry—nothing wrong. Miss F. is far too sharp for that. She plays one off against the other. Two strings to her bow.”

“I think you seem rather wide-awake yourself,” said the Major, laughing.

“Pretty well, thank you, sir. How are you?” replied Foker, utterly unshaken. “I’m not clever, maybe—but I’m downy. And friends say I know what time it is. Can I tell you the hour?”

“Oh, indeed,” said the Major, delighted. “You might be very useful. You’re a man of the world. And as such, I needn’t tell you that our family is most displeased with this absurd affair Arthur’s involved in.”

“Should think not,” said Foker. “Connexion not eligible. Too much beer drunk on the premises. No Irish need apply. That’s what I take to be your meaning.”

The Major nodded, though he wasn’t entirely sure what the meaning was. But he pressed on.

“What can you tell me about the Costigans?”

“Well,” said Foker, “the General enjoys a jovial bowl now and then. If I wanted to invest my money safely, I wouldn’t trust it to him. But he keeps a watchful eye on his daughter. Neither he nor she will stand for anything dishonourable. Pen’s attentions are the talk of the regiment. I hear all about it from Miss Rouncy—used to be close friends with her. She says Sir Derby Oaks has been hanging around Miss Fotheringay ever since his regiment came down. But Pen’s come in and cut him out lately—so mad has the Baronet become, he’s nearly proposed himself. Wish he would. Then we’d see which one Miss F. jumps at.”

“I suspected as much,” said the Major. “You’ve given me great pleasure, Mr. Foker. I wish I’d met you sooner.”

“Didn’t want to put in my oar,” said Foker. “Don’t speak till I’m asked. Then, if there’s no objection, I speak freely. Heard your man was sniffing around my servant—didn’t know what was going on until Miss Fotheringay and Miss Rouncy had a row over ostrich feathers. Then Miss R. told me everything.”

“So Miss Rouncy was the confidante?”

“Confidante? Indeed. Why, she’s twice as clever as Fotheringay—literary, educated. Miss F. can barely read.”

“She can write,” said the Major, remembering the letter in Pen’s pocket.

Foker let out a sardonic laugh. “He, he! Rouncy writes her letters—every single one. Since they quarrelled, she doesn’t know how to get on. Rouncy’s hand is beautiful. Miss F.’s? Well, when Bows isn’t around, it’s a disaster. Rouncy’s been setting her copies lately.”

“I suppose you know it well,” said the Major, archly.

Foker winked. “I’d give a great deal to have a specimen,” said the Major. “Could you spare one?”

“No, no—that’d be too bad,” said Foker. “But let me tell you—Miss F.’s writing ain’t terrible, I suppose. Only she had Rouncy write the first letter, and kept going. Mark my words: until they’re reconciled, the letters stop.”

“I hope they never reconcile,” said the Major, with genuine fervor. “And I can’t tell you how pleased I am to have met you. As a man of the world, you must see how disastrous this engagement is for Arthur’s future. We must free him from these schemers—father and daughter alike. Love on the lady’s side? Out of the question.”

“Love, indeed!” said Foker. “If Pen hadn’t two thousand a year when he came of age—”

“If Pen hadn’t what?” cried the Major, suddenly sitting upright.

“Two thousand a year. Doesn’t he?”

“My dear sir,” shrieked the Major, “thank you! Thank you! I begin to see now. Two thousand a year? Why, his mother has five hundred in the world. She’ll live to eighty, and Arthur hasn’t a shilling beyond what she allows.”

“What! He ain’t rich?”

“Not a penny more than I said.”

“And you’re not leaving him anything?”

The Major sighed. He’d spent every shilling on annuities. He wouldn’t leave Pen a thing. But he didn’t say so.

“How much do you think a major on half-pay can save?” he asked.

“Ah,” said Foker, “then these people have been mistaken. They thought he was wealthy. And you’ve made me the happiest man alive.”

“Sir to you,” said Foker, shaking hands with great cordiality.

As the Major climbed the stairs to his room, and Foker lit a cigar by the inn’s pillars, Arthur—ten miles away—was likely lying in bed, kissing a letter from Emily.

The next morning, before Foker drove off in his drag, the Major had managed to slip a letter from Miss Rouncy into his pocket-book. Let it be a lesson to all women: beware of overconfidence in handwriting.

In high spirits, Major Pendennis visited Doctor Portman at the Deanery and recounted his discoveries. Over breakfast, they could see Captain Costigan’s window—where poor Pen had once been plainly visible.

The Doctor raged against Mrs. Creed, the landlady, for hiding Sir Derby Oaks’s visits. He threatened excommunication.

But the Major, wise in the ways of battle, felt ready.

“I’m going to fight the dragon,” he declared.

“And I shrive you,” said the Doctor, “and bid good fortune go with you.”

Perhaps the Portmans and Miss Myra, seated with the Dean’s lady, glanced up more than once at the enemy’s window, hoping to catch sight of the duel.

The Major walked round, found Mrs. Creed’s door, and ascended to Captain Costigan’s apartment. Inside, he heard stamping and shouts of “Ha, ha!”

“It’s Sir Derby Oaks taking fencing lessons,” said the child guiding him. “Mondays, Wednesdays, Fridays.”

The Major knocked. A tall man emerged, foil in hand, mask dangling, glove on one hand.

Pendennis bowed. “I believe I have the honour of speaking to Captain Costigan. My name is Major Pendennis.”

Captain Costigan saluted with his foil. “Major, the honer is moine; I’m deloighted to see ye.”

The Major and Captain Costigan were seasoned soldiers, used to facing down enemies in the field—so it’s safe to say they kept their heads when Pendennis burst into the room like a man who’d just escaped from a duel with destiny. The rest of the party, however, might have been forgiven for losing their composure slightly. Miss Fotheringay, bless her slow heart, flushed up like a rose after a summer shower—probably because Sir Derby Oaks was glaring at her as if she’d stolen his last biscuit. And poor little Mr. Bows, the crooked old gentleman in the window seat (who had been trying to write theatre music until the fencing match upstairs made him give up and mutter about “dancing fools”), looked up eagerly, as though he’d just witnessed a miracle.

Then came the Major—well-booted, well-bowled, and full of the grace that only a man who’s spent decades pretending to be noble can master. “Me daughter—me friend, Mr. Bows—me gallant young pupil and friend, I may call ’um, Sir Derby Oaks,” announced Costigan, waving his hand like a general addressing a parade ground. “In one moment, Meejor, I’m your humble servant!”

And off he went—into the tiny bedroom next door—to perform the most dramatic grooming ritual since Cleopatra applied her makeup. With the speed of a man whose life depends on fashion, he brushed his lank hair (a relic of ancient times), tore off his old stock, slipped on a new one Emily had sewn herself, added a clean collar, and donned the brand-new coat ordered especially for Miss Fotheringay’s benefit night. All this took less than a minute. Then Sir Derby emerged, freshly encased in his shell-jacket, which clung to his broad frame like armor made for a giant baby. He looked magnificent—though perhaps not as magnificent as he thought he did. Miss Fotheringay probably admired him; Pen certainly did; and even the Major couldn’t help but notice.

Meanwhile, polite conversation had begun between the actress and the visitor. They exchanged the usual pleasantries about the weather—“Rather damp today,” said the Major, “but not without charm.” Then Costigan reappeared, resplendent in his new attire (which he called his “Shoot”).

“I needn’t apologoise to ye, Meejor,” he declared, “for receiving ye in me shirt-sleeves.”

“A soldier’s best duty is teaching a young one how to handle a sword,” replied the Major, bowing so low you could almost see the ceiling. “I remember hearing that you were quite skilled with one, Captain Costigan.”

“What? Ye’ve heard of Jack Costigan, Major?” the Irishman exclaimed, puffing out his chest like a rooster.

Indeed, the Major had. He’d asked his nephew Arthur about the fellow, and whether he knew anything else beyond what he’d been told. Whether he actually remembered Costigan from past days or not—well, that’s a mystery. But Major Pendennis was a man of honor and truth, and he insisted he’d met Captain Costigan before—at Sir Richard Strachan’s table in Walcheren—and had even heard him sing.

At this, Bows blinked. “Well now,” he muttered, “that’s a surprise.”

But the Major wasn’t done. “We’ll talk of these things another time,” he said, clearly wanting to avoid any deeper entanglement. “Today, I came to pay my respects to Miss Fotheringay.”

He bowed again—a bow so grand, so courtly, so full of theatrical flair, that if she’d been a duchess, she wouldn’t have gotten better treatment.

“I’ve heard of your performances from my nephew,” the Major continued, “who raves about you—though, of course, he’s only a boy, wild and enthusiastic, and we must take his opinions with a grain of salt. Still, I confess I wanted to judge for myself. And I must say—your performance delighted and astonished me. I’ve seen our finest actresses, and I truly believe you surpass them all. You are as majestic as Mrs. Siddons.”

“Faith, I always said so,” Costigan chimed in, winking at his daughter. “Major, take a chair.”

Milly sprang up, removed an unripped satin garment from the only available seat (as though it were sacred), and presented the chair with a curtsey so deep it nearly knocked her over.

“You are as pathetic as Miss O’Neill,” the Major went on, seating himself with great dignity. “Your singing reminded me of Mrs. Jordan in her prime—when we were young men, Captain Costigan. And your manner—ah, your manner reminds me of Mars.”

“Ah,” interjected Miss Emily, “there were two Mahers in Crow Street. Fanny was decent enough, but Biddy was no great shakes.”

“Sure, the Major means the god of war, Milly, my dear,” Costigan corrected gently.

“No, not exactly,” the Major replied, smiling warmly at Sir Derby Oaks, who had just returned in his shell-jacket. “Though Venus, I suppose, may forgive him for thinking about him.”

But Miss Fotheringay didn’t catch the joke. Nor did Sir Derby. In fact, he scowled harder than ever—like someone who’d just discovered the soup was lukewarm. His expression seemed to say: *What in the name of all that’s holy is this man doing here?*

The Major, however, wasn’t fazed. On the contrary—he found it delightful. *A rival!* he thought. *Excellent.* And he silently vowed that Sir Derby should not only be a rival—but a winner too—in this love affair that had already claimed both him and Pen.

“I fear I interrupted your fencing lesson,” the Major said, smoothing his way forward. “But my stay in Chatteris is brief, and I wished to greet my old campaigner, Captain Costigan, and meet a lady who has enchanted me from the stage.”

He paused. “I wasn’t the only one charmed last night, Miss Fotheringay—though I must call you so, though your family name is ancient and noble. There was a reverend friend of mine who went home raving about Ophelia. And I saw Sir Derby Oaks fling a bouquet—now, that’s a gesture no actress ever deserved more. Did you know those flowers are still sitting in a glass of water on the mantelpiece?”

“I am very fond of flowers,” Miss Fotheringay replied, giving Sir Derby a look that would have melted stone—had he been made of anything softer than granite.

But he remained unmoved. “Sweets to the sweet—isn’t that the line from the play?” the Major asked, trying to be charming.

“’Pon my life, I don’t know,” Sir Derby grumbled. “Very likely it is. I ain’t much of a literary man.”

“Is it possible?” the Major asked, feigning shock. “You donー’t inherit your father’s love of letters, then? He was a remarkable scholar—I had the honor of knowing him well.”

“Indeed,” Sir Derby muttered, giving a stiff nod.

“He saved my life,” the Major added.

“Did he now?” Miss Fotheringay gasped, rolling her eyes first at the Major, then at Sir Derby—with gratitude.

But Sir Derby barely blinked. If anything, he looked annoyed. As though the idea of his father saving the Major’s life was somehow an insult. “My father,” he said coldly, “was a very good doctor. I’m not in that line myself. I wish you good morning, sir. I’ve got an appointment—Cos, bye-bye—Miss Fotheringay, good morning.”

And with that, he stormed out of the room—despite the young lady’s pleading glances and sweet smiles. The clatter of his sabre echoed down the stairs, followed by furious curses directed at little Tom Creed, who was playing with a peg-top in the hallway. Sir Derby kicked it into the street with a curse.

The Major didn’t smile. Not even a little. “Monstrous handsome young man,” he said to Costigan. “As fine a soldier as I’ve ever seen.”

“A credit to the army and human nature in general,” Costigan agreed.

“A young man of refined manners, polite affabilitee, and princely fortune. His table is sumptuous—he’s adawr’d in the regiment—and he rides sixteen stone.”

“A perfect champion,” laughed the Major. “I have no doubt all the ladies admire him.”

“He’s very well,” Milly said, “in spite of his weight, while he’s young. But he’s no conversation.”

“He’s best on horseback,” Mr. Bows observed.

“Oh yes,” Milly replied, “he rode third in the steeplechase on Tareaways.” The Major began to wonder how such a woman could act so brilliantly yet speak so plainly.

Costigan, true to Irish hospitality, immediately offered refreshments. The Major, though not hungry—having just survived a Lord Mayor’s dinner—declared he’d like a biscuit and a glass of wine, “for I feel faint from long fasting.” He knew, of course, that small kindnesses flatter donors immensely.

“Some of the old Madara, Milly, love,” Costigan said, winking at his daughter.

She nodded, left the room, descended the stairs, and quietly summoned Tommy Creed. She gave him a coin and instructed him to buy a pint of Madara wine from the Grapes and sixpennyworth of sorted biscuits from the baker. And if he returned quickly, he’d get two biscuits for himself.

While Tommy was gone, Miss Costigan sat downstairs with Mrs. Creed, telling her landlady all about the Major being upstairs—a “nice, soft-spoken old gentleman; butter wouldn’t melt in his mouth”—and how Sir Derby had stormed out in a jealous rage. What should they do?

“She keeps the keys of the cellar, Major,” Costigan said, as Milly left.

“Upon my word, you have a beautiful butler,” the Major replied, gallantly. “No wonder the young fellows go mad for her. When we were their age, Captain Costigan, I think plain women would have done us just fine.”

“Faith, and ye may say that, sir,” Costigan said. “And lucky is the man who gets her. Ask my friend Bob Bows here whether Miss Fotheringay’s mind isn’t superior to her person, whether she doesn’t possess a cultivated intellect, a refined understanding, and an emiable disposition?”

“O of course,” Bows replied, rather dryly.

“Here comes Hebe blushing from the cellar,” Costigan said. “Don’t you think it’s time to go to rehearsal, Miss Hebe? You’ll be fined if you’re late.”

He gave her a look that said, *Let’s leave these two alone.*

Miss Hebe, looking lovely and cheerful, picked up her bonnet and shawl. Bows gathered his papers, hobbled across the room, and fetched his hat and cane.

“Must you go?” the Major asked. “Can’t you stay a few minutes longer, Miss Fotheringay? Before you leave, allow an old fellow to shake your hand. I am proud to have met you, and sincerely hope to be your friend.”

She curtsied deeply. The Major followed her to the door, squeezed her hand with paternal warmth, and watched her go.

Bows stared. *The lad’s relatives can’t really want to marry him to her,* he thought.

And so they departed.

Now, thought Major Pendennis, *time to negotiate.*

And Captain Costigan, once his daughter was gone, immediately seized the opportunity to drink the rest of the wine—gulp after gulp of Madeira from the Grapes, his hand shaking with eagerness.

The Major joined him at the table, lifted his glass, and drained it with a satisfied smack. “Capital Madeira, Captain Costigan,” he said. “Where do you get it? I drink to that charming creature in a bumper. Faith, I don’t wonder the men are wild about her. I’ve never seen such eyes—or such a grand manner. I’m sure she’s as intelligent as she is beautiful. And I have no doubt she’s as good as she is clever.”

“A good girl, sir—*a good girl, sir*,” Costigan repeated, delighted. “And I pledge a toast to her with all my heart. Shall I send for another pint? It’s handy by. No? Well, indeed, sir, ye may say she is a good girl—the pride and glory of her father—honest old Jack Costigan. The man who gets her will have a jewel for a wife, sir. And I drink his health, sir—you know who I mean, Major.”

“I’m not surprised young or old fall in love with her,” the Major said. “And frankly, I must tell you, though I was angry with my nephew Arthur when I heard of his passion—now I’ve seen the lady, I can forgive him anything. By George, I’d enter the race myself—if I weren’t an old fellow and poor.”

“And no better man, Major,” Costigan cried, enraptured. “Your friendship delights me. Your admiration for my girl brings tears to my eyes—*tears, sir—manlee tears*—and when she leaves me humble home for your more splendid mansion, I hope she’ll keep a place for her poor old father—poor old Jack Costigan.”

He spoke with such emotion that tears welled in his bloodshot eyes.

“Your sentiments do you honour,” the Major said. “But, Captain Costigan, I can’t help smiling at one thing you just said.”

“And what’s that, sir?” Jack asked, still in heroic mode.

“You mentioned our splendid mansion—my sister’s house, I mean.”

“I mane the park and mansion of Arthur Pendennis, Esquire, of Fairoaks Park,” Costigan declared, with dignity. “Whom I hope to see a Member of Parliament for Clavering, when he is of age to take that responsible station.”

The Major smiled. He recognized the arrow—it was one he’d shot himself. He’d planted the idea in Pen’s head about running for Parliament. And now the boy had been bragging about it to Costigan and his beloved.

“Fairoaks Park, my dear sir,” the Major said. “Do you know our history? We’re of ancient lineage, certainly—but I began life with scarcely enough money to buy my commission. My eldest brother was a country apothecary—who made every shilling he died with from his pestle and mortar.”

“I have consented to waive that objection, sir,” Costigan said, magnificently, “in consideration of the known respectability of your family.”

“Curse your impudence,” the Major thought—but only smiled and bowed.

“The Costigans have met with misfortunes too,” Costigan continued. “Our house of Castle Costigan is by no means what it was. I’ve known honest apothecaries, sir—and there are some in Dublin who’ve dined at the Lord Lieutenant’s table.”

“You’re very kind to give us the benefit of your charity,” the Major said. “But let me say—this isn’t the issue. You spoke of my nephew as heir to Fairoaks Park and all that goes with it.”

“Funded property, I’ve no doubt, Meejor—and something handsome eventually from yourself.”

“My good sir,” the Major said, rising, “I tell you the boy is the son of a country apothecary. When he comes of age, he won’t have a shilling.”

“Pooh, Major, you’re laughing at me,” Costigan said. “Me young friend, I make no doubt, is heir to two thousand pounds a year.”

“Two thousand fiddlesticks!” the Major exploded. “Has the boy been humbugging you? It’s not his habit. Upon my word and honour, as a gentleman and executor of my brother’s will, he left little more than five hundred a year.”

“And with aconomy,” Costigan insisted, “a handsome sum too. Faith, I’ve known a man drink his clar’t and drive his coach-and-four on five hundred a year and strict aconomy in Ireland. We’ll manage on it—trust Jack Costigan for that.”

“My dear Captain Costigan,” the Major said, “I give you my word—my brother left nothing to his son. Not a shilling.”

“Are ye joking with me, Meejor Pendennis?” Costigan roared. “Are ye thrifling with the feelings of a father and a gentleman?”

“I am telling you the honest truth,” the Major said calmly. “Every shilling my brother had, he left to his widow—with a partial reversion to the boy. But she’s young and may marry if he offends her—or she may outlive him. And I ask you, as a gentleman, what allowance can my sister make to her son from five hundred a year—enough to maintain himself and your daughter in the rank befitting such an accomplished young lady?”

“Am I to understand,” Costigan thundered, “that the young gentleman—your nephew—whom I have fostered and cherished as the son of me bosom—is an imposther who has been thrifling with the affections of me beloved child?”

“Have you yourself been working upon the young man’s susceptible nature to injuice him to break off an engagement—and with it me adored Emily’s heart? Have a care, sir, how you thrifle with the honour of John Costigan. If I thought any mortal man meant to do so, by heavens, I’d have his blood—were he old or young.”

“Mr. Costigan!” the Major cried.

“Mr. Costigan can protect his own and his daughter’s honour,” the other snapped. “Look at that chest of drawers—it contains heaps of letters that that viper has addressed to that innocent child. Promises enough to fill a bandbox. And when I drag the scoundrel before the Courts of Law, and show up his perjury and dishonour, I have another remedy in yondther mahogany case—ye mark me words, Major Pendennis—with any individual who has counselled your nephew to insult a soldier and a gentleman. What? Me daughter to be jilted, and me grey hairs dishonoured by an apothecary’s son? By the laws of Heaven, I’d like to see the man who dares do it.”

“I am to understand,” the Major said, cool as ice, “that you threaten first to publish the letters of an eighteen-year-old boy to a woman of twenty-eight—and then to do me the honour of calling me out?”

“You have described my intentions perfectly, Meejor Pendennis,” Costigan replied, pulling his whiskers over his chin.

“Well, well,” the Major said, “these shall be matters for future arrangements. But before we come to powder and ball, my good sir—do have the kindness to consider: in what earthly way have I injured you? I’ve told you my nephew is dependent on his mother, who has barely five hundred a year.”

“I have my own opinion of that assertion,” Costigan said.

“Will you go to my sister’s lawyers, Messrs. Tatham, and satisfy yourself?”

“I decline to meet those gentlemen,” Costigan said, disturbed.

“If it’s as you say, I’ve been atrociously deceived—and I’ll be revenged.”

“Is it my nephew?” the Major asked, standing up and putting on his hat.

“Did he ever tell you he had two thousand a year? If he did, I’m mistaken in the boy. Lying is not in our family. I don’t think my brother’s son has learned it yet. Try to consider—have you not deceived yourself? Or adopted exaggerated reports from hearsay?”

“As for me, sir,” the Major said, “you are at liberty to understand I am not afraid of all the Costigans in Ireland. I know how to defend myself against any threat. I come here as the boy’s guardian to protest against a marriage—absurd and unequal—that would bring poverty and misery. And in preventing it, I believe I am doing your daughter’s honour as much as my own family’s. And prevent it I will, by every means in my power. There. I have said my say.”

“But I have not said mine, Major Pendennis,” Costigan said, eyes blazing. “And ye shall hear more from me.”

“’Sdeath, sir, what do you mean?” the Major asked, turning back at the door.

“Ye said,” Costigan said, slowly and gravely, “that ye were at the George Hotel, I think.”

“A friend shall wait upon ye there before ye leave town, sir.”

“Let him make haste, Mr. Costigan,” the Major shouted, almost beside himself. “I wish you a good morning, sir.”

And Captain Costigan bowed a magnificent, defiant bow over the landing as the Major retreated down the stairs—leaving behind a silence thick with threats, tears, and the lingering scent of Madeira.

CHAPTER XII. In Which a Shooting Match Is Proposed—But Not Quite as Planned

Let us now turn our attention to Mr. Garbetts, Principal Tragedian, a man of considerable physical presence and even more considerable appetite for rum, who was also known for his theatrical flair, his love of good company, and his unfortunate habit of getting into scrapes that required the intervention of equally reckless friends. He was, in short, the kind of fellow you’d find at the Magpie Hotel on a Thursday evening—loud, dramatic, and always ready to lend a hand (or a signature) when a bill needed backing.

Mr. Garbetts and Captain Costigan were bosom companions in both conviviality and financial misadventure. They had shared many a bottle, many a debt, and many a whispered plot involving the transfer of one another’s names onto promissory notes. Their friendship was strong, though not without its complications—chiefly because Miss Fotheringay, the Captain’s daughter, did not care for Mr. Garbetts, and Mrs. Garbetts, being a woman of delicate sensibilities, took great exception to her husband’s frequent visits to the Costigan household. The truth of the matter was that Garbetts had once courted Miss Fotheringay with all the earnestness of a Shakespearean lover—only to be rebuffed with the briskness of a stage mother dismissing a suitor. That slight had left a lingering sore spot, but we needn’t dwell on it; let’s just say that Garbetts was summoned by Captain Costigan shortly after young Arthur Pendennis and Miss Costigan’s friend Mr. Bows had departed from the house.

“Run, Tommy,” said the Captain to the small boy who served as their personal courier, “and fetch Mr. Garbetts from his lodgings above the tripe shop—ye know the place—and tell ’em to send two hot glasses of whisky-and-water from the Grapes.”

Tommy obeyed, and soon enough Garbetts arrived, puffing slightly from the climb, accompanied by the scent of spirits and the sound of his own booming voice.

Captain Costigan did not go into the full details of what had transpired earlier—though the reader is well aware of them. Instead, he poured out a generous measure of whisky-and-water and began composing a letter of such fiery intensity that it could have set a barn ablaze if it had been delivered to its intended recipient.

This missive, addressed to Major Pendennis, demanded that the gentleman either consent immediately to the marriage between Arthur Pendennis and Miss Fotheringay—or else offer the satisfaction due between gentlemen of honour. Should the Major refuse, the Captain threatened to administer justice himself, using a horsewhip as his instrument of choice. The tone was, naturally, rather grandiose, and the letter was sealed with the family silver seal—the only piece of ancestral plate the Captain possessed, which he kept wrapped in velvet like a sacred relic.

Garbetts was then dispatched with this letter and the weighty responsibility of delivering it. Before he left, the Captain gripped his arm with solemn gravity and declared, “May Heaven bless ye, General.”

Then, once the messenger was gone, Costigan retrieved his ancient duelling pistols—flint-locked relics that had seen action in Dublin’s most famous duels—and inspected them with the care of a man preparing for battle. Satisfied, he pulled out all of Arthur Pendennis’s letters and poems from the drawer, those tender missives he had carefully hoarded as proof of affection and betrayal alike. He read them over again, perhaps to steel himself for the confrontation ahead.

But minutes later, Garbetts returned—not with news of triumph, but with a face as long as a funeral procession.

“Ye’ve seen ’um?” asked the Captain, eyes gleaming with anticipation.

“Yes,” replied Garbetts.

“And when is it for?” asked Costigan, testing the lock of one pistol and bringing it up to his eye—what he called “his bloodshot orb.”

“What do you mean—‘when is it for’?” asked Garbetts, confused.

“The meeting, my dear fellow!” roared Costigan. “The duel!”

“You don’t mean to say you want mortal combat?” said Garbetts, recoiling as if struck. “You’re not serious, Captain?”

“What the devil else do I mean?” bellowed Costigan. “I want to shoot the man who has trampled upon my honor—or else I’ll fall dead myself upon the sod!”

“I’ll not carry challenges,” said Garbetts firmly. “I’m a family man. I have children. I will have nothing to do with pistols.” And with that, he flung the letter down—its elaborate superscription and smudged seal landing dramatically on the floor.

Costigan stared in disbelief. “Ye don’t mean to say ye saw ’um and didn’t give ’um the letter?”

“I saw him,” said Garbetts, “but I couldn’t speak with him.”

“And why not?”

“There was one there I cared not to meet,” said Garbetts in a voice like a tombstone. “And neither would you.”

“The minion Tatham was there?” cried Costigan.

“The cowardly scoundrel!” he roared. “He’s frightened already and going to swear peace against me.”

“I’ll have nothing to do with fighting,” repeated Garbetts, “and I wish I hadn’t seen Tatham nor that bit of——”

“Hold your tongue, Bob Acres!” snapped Costigan, quoting Sir Lucius O’Trigger, a role he had played with distinction both on and off the stage. After some heated exchange, the two parted ways—neither particularly pleased with the other.

Now, as the reader may have guessed, we cannot reproduce the exact wording of the letter, for a very simple reason: it was never opened. Major Pendennis never received it. Why? Because Garbetts refused to deliver it—and no one else dared.

When Miss Costigan returned home from rehearsal, escorted by the ever-present Mr. Bows, she found her father pacing wildly about the room, reeking of whisky-and-water and despair. The Pendennis papers lay scattered across the table, beside empty goblets and a teaspoon that had clearly seen better days.

As Emily entered, her father seized her in a crushing embrace. “Prepare yourself, me child, me blessed child!” he wailed, tears streaming down his cheeks. “It’s all over!”

“Ye’re tipsy again, Papa,” said Emily, pushing him away gently. “Ye promised not to take spirits before dinner.”

“It’s to forget me sorrows,” he sobbed. “To drown me care!”

“Your care takes a deal of drowning, Captain dear,” remarked Bows, mimicking Costigan’s accent. “What’s happened? Has that soft-spoken gentleman in the wig been vexing you?”

“The oily miscreant!” roared Costigan. “I’ll have his blood!”

Miss Milly, meanwhile, had retreated to her room to remove her bonnet and shawl. She came back looking perfectly composed—unlike her father, who was now trembling with rage and grief. In her hands she carried a pair of white satin shoes, which she intended to polish with bread crumbs in preparation for her performance as Ophelia that coming Tuesday.

She glanced at the papers on the table, paused, then decided against asking any questions. Instead, she selected a suitable piece of bread from the cupboard and sat down at the table with her shoes and her task.

“Papa,” she said eventually, “what have ye got all these letters and poetry of Master Arthur’s out for? Sure ye don’t want to read that nonsense again?”

“O Emilee!” cried Costigan, “that boy whom I loved as the boy of mee bosom is only a scoundrel, a deceiver! A liar!”

He turned to Bows, who looked on with mild concern.

“He’s been acting the part of a viper in this house, a traitor in this familee!” Costigan continued. “I tell ye—he’s no better than an impostor!”

“What has he done, Papa?” asked Emily calmly.

“Done? He has deceived us in the most atrocious manner!” said Costigan. “He pretended to be a man of property—two thousand a year, mind you—but it turns out he’s a pauper! A dependent on his mother’s five hundred pounds! How dare he ask ye to marry into a family that can’t provide for ye?”

Emily looked thoughtful, polishing her shoe slowly. “Sure, if he’s no money, there’s no use marrying him, Papa.”

“Why did the villain say he was rich?” asked Costigan.

“The poor fellow always said he was poor,” replied Emily. “’Twas you who insisted he was rich—and made me agree to take him.”

“He should have been explicit,” said Costigan. “A young man who rides a blood mare and gives presents of shawls and bracelets is an impostor if he has no money. As for his uncle—bedad, I’ll pull off his wig whenever I see ’um!”

Bows chuckled. “Bedad, you may send somebody else with the message. I’m a fiddler, not a fighting man.”

“Pooh, you’ve no spirit!” roared Costigan. “I’ll be my own second. I’ll take my case of pistols and shoot ’um in the Coffee-room of the George!”

“And so poor Arthur has no money?” sighed Emily.

“Poor lad, he was a good lad too,” she added softly. “Wild and talking nonsense with his verses and pothry, but a brave, generous boy. And indeed, I liked him—and he liked me too.”

“Why don’t you marry him if you like him so?” asked Bows, a little sharply.

“He is not more than ten years younger than you are,” he went on. “His mother may relent. You might live at Fairoaks Park. Why not go and be a lady? I could keep playing the fiddle, and the General live on his half-pay. Why don’t you marry him? You know he likes you.”

“There’s others that like me as well,” said Miss Milly, still polishing. “That have no money and that’s old enough.”

“Yes, d—— it,” said Bows, cursing under his breath. “Old enough and poor enough and fools enough for anything.”

“There’s old fools, and young fools too,” retorted Emily, giving him a knowing look. “You’ve often said so, you silly man.”

“If Pendennis has not enough money to live upon, it’s folly to talk about marrying him,” she concluded. “And that’s the long and short of it.”

“And the boy?” asked Bows.

“By Jove!” he exclaimed. “You throw a man away like an old glove, Miss Costigan.”

“I don’t know what you mean,” she replied placidly, turning to the second shoe. “If he had half of the two thousand a year that Papa gave him, or even half of that, I would marry him. But what’s the good of taking on with a beggar? We’re poor enough already. There’s no use in my going to live with an old lady that’s testy and cross, maybe, and would grudge me every morsel of meat.”

(Sure, it’s near dinner time, and Suky not laid the cloth yet.)

“And then,” she added simply, “suppose there was a family? Why, Papa, we shouldn’t be as well off as we are now.”

“’Deed, then, you wouldn’t, Milly dear,” answered her father.

“And there’s an end to all the fine talk about Mrs. Arthur Pendennis of Fairoaks Park—the member of Parliament’s lady,” said Milly with a laugh. “Pretty carriages and horses we should have to ride! That you were always talking about, Papa! But it’s always the same. If a man looked at me, you fancied he was going to marry me; and if he had a good coat, you fancied he was as rich as Croesus.”

“Well, call ’um what ye like,” said Bows. “But it’s a fact now that Papa has married me these eight years a score of times. Wasn’t I to be my Lady Poldoody of Oystherstown Castle? Then there was the Navy Captain at Portsmouth, and the old surgeon at Norwich, and the Methodist preacher here last year, and who knows how many more? Well, I bet a penny, with all your scheming, I shall die Milly Costigan at last.”

So poor little Arthur has no money? Stop and take dinner, Bows; we’ve a beautiful beef-steak pudding.”

Bows watched her closely. “I wonder whether she is on with Sir Derby Oaks,” he thought. “The dodges of women beat all comprehension. And I am sure she wouldn’t let the lad off so easily if she hadn’t some other scheme on hand.”

It must be said that Miss Fotheringay, though quiet and not much given to literary discourse, could hold her own in domestic matters. She wasn’t romantic, nor was she deeply versed in poetry—she hadn’t opened a Shakespeare since leaving the stage, and frankly didn’t understand it. But when it came to puddings, needlework, or family affairs, she was as sharp as a tack. Unmoved by passion or imagination, she kept her head cool where others lost theirs.

Over dinner, Costigan tried to argue that the Major’s statement about Pen’s finances was false—a trick to make them break off the match. But Miss Milly would hear none of it. “It’s not deceit on their side,” she said. “It’s deception on yours, Papa. Poor little Pen didn’t try to fool us. It was you who deceived yourself.”

She ate her dinner with great relish, while Bows watched her with a mixture of admiration and contempt.

Afterward, they discussed how best to end the affair. Costigan’s idea of pulling the Major’s nose faded with the last drop of whisky-and-water. He became submissive to his daughter, ready to follow her lead.

“What’ll we say to the lad if he stays steady and we break off?” asked Costigan.

“What? Don’t you know how to throw a man over?” said Bows. “Ask a woman to tell you?”

Miss Fotheringay explained it simply: “Papa writes to Arthur, asks about settlements and income. Arthur replies honestly. Then papa writes back and says it’s not enough. The match ends.”

“And of course,” added Bows, “you enclose a parting line saying you’ll always regard him as a brother.”

“Of course,” said Milly. “And so I shall. He’s a most worthy young man, I’m sure.”

Then she reached into a drawer, pulled out a bundle of newspapers—County Chronicle and Chatteris Champion—and extracted the pages where Arthur had written glowing verses about her portrayal of Imogen. She folded the letters and poems neatly, tied them with string like a parcel of sugar, and placed them aside.

Not a tremor passed through her. No sigh, no tear. She had just packed away the heart of a young man who had poured his soul into those pages—hours of longing, devotion, sleepless nights—all bundled up like grocery.

She then sat down to make tea, her heart perfectly calm, while far away, Arthur Pendennis was still dreaming of her.

At Fairoaks, the household was beginning to wonder where Major Pendennis had gotten to. Dr. Portman and his wife, on their way back to Clavering, stopped by Helen’s lodge gate with a short note from the Major—just one of those polite little missives that say more than they mean. It read something like: “Dear Helen—staying in Chatteris another day. Must talk with Messrs. Tatham about some business. Will be home soon. Love.” No mention of the morning’s drama, naturally. The note had been written mid-battle, when the Major was still reeling from the verbal thrashing he’d just taken. He didn’t feel like going into town while his uncle was around—especially not if the man in the polished boots might be lurking somewhere near the Dean’s grass-plat, spying on him as he tried to flirt in Miss Costigan’s drawing-room. Pen wasn’t exactly shy, but he was deeply, profoundly embarrassed by the idea of being seen making eyes at a girl by someone who thought he should be wearing a wig and reading the Bible.

Love, for Pen, was a silent affair—except for the eyes, which did all the shouting. And even then, it was only in private. In public? Forget it.
