Love-talk, especially when overheard by strangers, is the most absurd kind of nonsense in existence. Try reading your old love letters again, dear reader—you’ll see what we mean. Those once-magical lines now look like gibberish scribbled by a madman. “I shall never forget you,” says one. “You are my everything.” But really? You probably forgot them ten minutes after writing them. Yet there was a time when you’d have kissed those very words with reverence, lived on them like bread, and felt utterly lost until the next dose arrived. That’s why we’re not publishing any of Pen’s poems or love notes. They’re too sweet, too dramatic, too full of teenage delusion. Let young ladies keep their own romantic dreams intact. We’ll leave these early effusions tied up in newspaper bundles with Miss Fotheringay’s string and sealed with Captain Costigan’s silver seal. For posterity. And discretion.

The Major left Captain Costigan’s house in such a state of rage that he looked like a volcano about to erupt. “The impudent, bog-trotting scamp!” he muttered under his breath. “Dare threaten me? Say I’m a coward? Send me a challenge? If the fool can find a gentleman to carry it, I’ll give him the fight of his life. Psha! What would people say if I fought a drunken mountebank over an actress in a barn?” So when Dr. Portman asked how the “dragon-slaying” had gone, the Major merely said it was unpleasant and far from over. He begged the doctor and his wife to keep quiet about it—no gossip at Fairoaks—and sent off the note we’ve already mentioned. Then he stomped back to his hotel, where he vented his fury on Mr. Morgan, his valet, “dammin’ and cussin’ upstairs and downstairs,” as Morgan later told Mr. Foker’s man during dinner in the servants’ room at the George.

The news spread fast. Mr. Foker, having just finished breakfast (it was two o’clock), remembered he wanted to know how the showdown between his two friends had gone. He checked the Major’s room number and, wrapped in his brocade dressing-gown and topped with an embroidered skull-cap (a gift from a devoted mother), marched over and knocked.

Inside, the Major was busy with papers and red tape, discussing a lease for the widow’s property with old Mr. Tatham, the family lawyer. Seeing the visitor, the modest youth Foker hesitated. “Oh, you’re busy—call another time,” he said, backing away.

But the Major insisted. “Come in, come in!” he said with a smile. So Foker removed his tarboosh (a fez, though no one called it that), bowed grandly, and sat down in an armchair, spreading out his crimson robes like a peacock showing off its tail feathers. “You seem to like my dressing-gown, sir,” he said to Mr. Tatham. “A pretty thing, isn’t it? Neat, but not gaudy. And how do you do, Major Pendennis, sir? How does the world treat you?”

There was something about Foker’s presence—his charm, his ridiculousness—that could cheer up even a monk. It smoothed the wrinkles beneath the Major’s hairline. “I’ve had an interview with that Irishman,” he said. “And it was… not satisfactory. He refuses to believe my nephew is poor. Says we’re both liars. And before I left, he had the nerve to suggest I was a coward. I thought, when I heard the knock, that you might be the gentleman carrying a challenge from Mr. Costigan. That’s how the world treats me, Mr. Foker.”

“Not the Irishman—the actress’s father?” gasped Mr. Tatham, who disliked the theatre and considered actors morally suspect.

“The very man. Haven’t you heard what a fool my nephew has made of himself over the girl?” Mr. Tatham hadn’t—he never set foot in a theatre. So the Major recounted the whole story, while Foker added comments in his usual breezy fashion. Tatham was horrified. Why hadn’t Mrs. Pendennis married a respectable man—like himself, a widower? Why had she raised this boy to ruin?

As for Miss Costigan—well, her profession spoke for itself. “I’ve known some decent folk in the booths,” Foker interjected. “They call it the Temple of the Muses.”

“Perhaps,” said Tatham, “but the father—I know him personally. Wine-bibber, idler, billiard-hall regular, and a notorious bankrupt. And here’s the kicker: we’ve got a writ against him and another actor—Mr. Garbetts—for a bill given to Mr. Skinner, a fine Quaker grocer and spirit merchant. Costigan came crying into Skinner’s shop, begging for mercy. We haven’t pursued it—neither man was worth the cost of powder and shot.”

Just then, a third knock came. In walked a shabby-looking man in a braided frock coat, holding a letter with a large, blotchy red seal. “May I speak with Major Pendennis privately?” he began. “I have a message from Captain Costigan.”

But then he froze. His face turned as pale as milk. He saw Mr. Tatham. “Hullo, Garbetts, speak up!” cried Foker, delighted.

“Why, bless my soul—it’s the other party to the bill!” said Tatham.

“Stop!” said Garbetts, gasping. Then he fled, like a rabbit caught in a spotlight.

Everyone burst out laughing. The Major laughed. Foker laughed. Even the solemn lawyer chuckled. “I don’t think there’ll be any fight, Major,” said Foker, mimicking a tragedian. “If there is, the old gentleman—your name Tatham?—may send the bailiffs to separate the men.” Tatham promised he would.

The Major was actually relieved. “It seems to me, sir,” he said to Foker, “that you always arrive just in time to put me in good humour.”

And indeed, Foker was about to become the hero of the Pendennis family that day. He’d been invited into Captain Costigan’s lodgings, so he decided to pay a visit and hear the General’s side of the story. But Costigan wasn’t home—he’d gone to the Magpie Hotel, where he was doubtless bragging about wanting to murder a certain ruffian. He wasn’t just brave—he knew it, and liked to show it off.

But Miss Fotheringay was there, washing tea cups while Mr. Bows sat across from her. “Just done breakfast, I see—how do?” said Foker, popping his head in.

“Get out, you funny little man,” she snapped.

“I mean *come in*,” he replied. “Here we are!” And with that, he folded his arms and began spinning his head around like Harlequin breaking free from his cocoon. Miss Fotheringay roared with laughter. One wink from Foker could make her laugh; Bows’s bitterest joke couldn’t even get a smirk.

When the spin ended, Foker dropped to one knee and kissed her hand. “You’re the drollest little man,” she said, giving him a hearty slap.

Pen would have died if anyone slapped him. He trembled just kissing her hand.

After the preliminaries, the three began chatting. Foker recounted the scene with Garbetts, revealing just how furious Costigan had been. He defended the Major’s character fiercely—calling him a “tip-top swell,” a man of impeccable honesty, and certainly not someone who’d deceive a lovely young woman like Miss Fotheringay.

He touched lightly on marriage, though he couldn’t help hinting that he thought Pen was a bit of a dreamer. “I knew it wouldn’t do, Miss Foth,” he said, nodding wisely. “Couldn’t do. Didn’t like to put my hand in the bag, but I knew it couldn’t do. Too young. Too green. A deal too green. And now he’s poor as Job. Can’t have him at no price, can she, Mr. Bo?”

“Indeed, he’s a nice poor boy,” said Miss Fotheringay, sadly.

“Poor little beggar,” said Bows, hands in pockets, stealing a glance at her. Maybe he was wondering how women play with men—coax them, win them, then drop them.

But Bows admitted the match was absurd. Miss Costigan agreed—only she couldn’t turn down two thousand a year. “It all comes of believing Papa’s silly stories,” she said. “Faith, I’ll choose for meself another time.” And perhaps, right then, Lieutenant Sir Derby Oaks flashed through her mind.

After praising the Major—“smelling of lavender, neat as a pin”—Foker suddenly suggested they all meet him for dinner that evening at his apartment at the George. “He agreed to dine with me,” he said. “And after the—after the little shindy this morning, in which I must say the General was wrong, it would look kind, you know. I know the Major fell in love with you, Miss Foth—he said so.”

“So she may be Mrs. Pendennis still,” sneered Bows.

“No, thank you, Mr. F.—I’ve dined.”

“Sure, that was at three o’clock,” said Miss Costigan, “and I can’t go without you.”

“We’ll have lobster-salad and champagne,” said Foker, who couldn’t parse Latin or do long division.

Now, for lobster-salad and champagne—especially served by the little monster who could barely spell—Miss Costigan would have walked through fire. So at seven o’clock, Major Pendennis found himself seated at a table with Mr. Bows, a professional fiddler, and Miss Costigan—whose father had nearly blown his brains out just hours before.

To complete the miracle, Foker sent Stoopid to fetch Costigan from the Magpie, where the General was singing a sentimental ballad. When he arrived, he found his daughter and Bows at the table. He was stunned. The Major laughed and shook his hand warmly. The General was quite drunk and had already been sobbing over his song before joining the party. He burst into tears more than once, calling the Major his “dearest friend.”

Stoopid and Foker walked him home. The Major gallantly gave his arm to Miss Costigan.

The next day, the Major returned to Fairoaks, saying nothing about the previous night. He invited Mr. Smirke to dinner, and anyone watching might have noticed something odd—his usual joviality seemed forced, his manners unusually careful. He gave Pen a firm “God bless you” before bed, and as they parted, he seemed about to say something to Mrs. Pendennis—but thought better of it. Better not disturb her sleep.

Next morning, he was down in the breakfast room earlier than usual, greeting everyone with great warmth. The post arrived just as the meal ended. John, the old servant, dumped the mail on the table. The Major watched Pen pick up his letter. Arthur blushed and set it down quickly. He knew the handwriting—it was Costigan’s. And he didn’t want to read it in front of everyone.

The Major knew the letter too. He’d posted it himself the day before. He sent little Laura out of the room—she hated him—and took Mrs. Pendennis’s hand. With a meaningful look, he pointed to the letter under the newspaper Pen was pretending to read.

“Will you come into the drawing-room?” he asked. “I want to speak to you.”

She followed, puzzled.

“What is it?” she whispered nervously.

“The affair is over,” said the Major. “He’s received his dismissal. I dictated it myself yesterday. There’s a few lines from the lady, too, bidding him farewell. It’s all over.”

Helen ran back to the dining room. Pen had snatched up the letter the moment they were gone. He read it with a blank stare. It said what the Major had said: Costigan was grateful for Arthur’s kindness, but had just learned of his financial situation. Marriage was impossible. Given the age difference, a future union was unthinkable. With deep regret, he bid Arthur farewell and asked him to stop visiting.

Enclosed was a note from Miss Costigan. She agreed with her father. She was older than Arthur, and an engagement was not feasible. She would always be grateful for his kindness, and hoped to remain friends. But for now, until the pain passed, they should not meet.

Pen read it mechanically. He looked up, wild-eyed, seeing his mother and uncle watching him with sorrow. Helen’s face was full of maternal concern.

“What—what is this?” he stammered. “It’s a joke. This isn’t her writing. Some servant wrote this. Who’s playing tricks on me?”

“It came in her father’s envelope,” said the Major. “Those letters you got before weren’t in her hand. That one is.”

“How do you know?” Pen snapped.

“I saw her write it,” the Major replied. Pen jumped up. His mother stepped forward and took his hand. He pushed her away.

“How could you see her? How dare you interfere between me and her? What have I ever done to you? Oh, it’s not true! It’s not true!” he shouted.

“She can’t have done it of her own accord,” he cried. “She can’t mean it. She’s pledged to me. Who’s lied to her?”

“Lies aren’t told in our family, Arthur,” said the Major. “I told her the truth—that you have no money. Her father had told her you were rich. When she learned the truth, she withdrew—without any pressure from me. She was right. She’s ten years older than you. She’s not suited to be your wife. Look at that handwriting—ask yourself, is such a woman fit to be your mother’s companion?”

“I’ll hear it from her,” said Arthur, crumpling the paper. “I’ll ask her to marry me. Once more. No one shall stop me.”

“What, a woman who spells affection with one ‘f’? Nonsense, sir. Be a man. Remember your mother is a lady. She was never meant to associate with that tipsy swindler or his daughter. Be a man. Forget her. As she has forgotten you.”

“Be a man and comfort your mother, my Arthur,” Helen said, embracing him.

Seeing them both shaken, the Major quietly left the room, closing the door behind him. He’d won. He’d even brought Pen’s love letters back in his portmanteau—returning them to Costigan with a little promissory note, which he settled with Mr. Tatham.

Pen rushed to Chatteris that day, desperate to see Miss Fotheringay. But she wasn’t there. He left a letter, enclosed to her father. It was returned. Costigan begged all correspondence cease. After a few more attempts, the General cut Pen off entirely. He ignored him in the street.

One day, walking with Foker, Arthur saw Emily on her father’s arm. She passed without a nod. Foker felt Pen trembling beside him.

His uncle wanted him to travel. His mother urged it too—Arthur was ill, suffering badly. But he refused. “I won’t go,” he said flatly. “I won’t obey.” His mother was too fond, his uncle too wise, to force him.

Whenever Miss Fotheringay performed, he rode to the Chatteris Theatre. One night, the house was nearly empty. The manager refunded their money. Pen went home, went to bed at eight, and developed a fever.

“If this goes on,” the Major thought, “I’ll have to go fetch the girl myself.”

As for Pen, he believed he was dying.

We won’t describe his feelings. We won’t bore you with a journal of despair. Have not others been rejected in love? Yes. But few die of it.

CHAPTER XIV. In Which Miss Fotheringay Lands a London Gig—And Poor Pen Finds Himself Left Behind

It was not long after the events previously recounted that Mr. Manager Bingley, in all his theatrical glory, took to the stage of Chatteris’s most illustrious theatre (which, for the sake of discretion and to avoid offending any local susceptibilities, we shall hereafter refer to as "the Museum") to perform the role of Rolla in *Pizarro*. The audience, however, was so thin it looked as though the play had been advertised as a fire drill rather than a drama. There were more empty seats than spectators, and poor Arthur Pendennis found himself occupying nearly the entire box section, like a lone sentry guarding a desert fort.

He sat there, eyes bloodshot from too much love and too little sleep, leaning over the railing like a man who’d just escaped from a sanitarium and hadn’t yet decided whether to return. He stared fixedly at the stage, where the world spun on in its usual fashion—Spaniards marched, Peruvians fell, priests debated fate, virgins of the sun blinked dramatically—but none of it registered. All he saw was Cora Fotheringay. When she wasn’t on stage, he saw nothing at all. Not even the sky, or the moon, or the fact that he might be developing a permanent hunchback from hunching forward in despair.

Later, he confessed that he was amazed he didn’t pull out a pistol and shoot her on the spot. “I was mad,” he said, “with love, rage, and the kind of despair only a young man with no prospects can truly feel.” Had it not been for his mother—the quiet, watchful, never-asking-questions sort of woman who somehow knew everything without being told—he might have ended up behind bars, or worse, hanging from the gallows outside Chatteris jail, leaving behind a single, half-written letter addressed to “My Beloved Cora.”

But no. He merely sat. And stared. And suffered.

Then, like a bolt of lightning through a dull evening, Cora entered. She was radiant—dressed in white, draped in leopard skin, wearing a golden sun across her chest, and adorned with bracelets so flashy they could have blinded a lesser mortal. She delivered her lines with the precision of a well-oiled machine, but her eyes—those same eyes that had once shattered Pendennis’s soul—rolled about the stage like twin moons, unbothered by anyone in particular.

Not him. Not even close.

And yet, he didn’t notice the two gentlemen who had just arrived in Box No. 3—nor did he realize that the entire cast had suddenly transformed into a group of desperate performers trying to impress someone far more important than the audience.

The first act had been a disaster. The actors yawned through their lines, whispered insults during pauses, and generally behaved as though they were rehearsing for a funeral. Even Bingley seemed bored. Mrs. Bingley muttered under her breath like a disgruntled housewife. Then—*boom*—everything changed.

Mrs. Bingley began bellowing like a bull elephant caught in a thunderstorm. Bingley leapt about the stage, screaming like a startled dean. Garbetts swaggered. Rowkins scowled. Miss Rouncy flounced. Everyone, it seemed, was suddenly trying to win the favor of the mysterious pair in Box No. 3.

One was a small, grey-haired man in black, with a face that suggested he’d once been a butcher and still liked to make jokes about meat. The other was a spectacle: tall, portly, with a nose like a hook and hair that curled like a lion’s mane. His coat was braided with gold frogs, his waistcoat layered like a sandwich of wealth, and his fingers glittered with rings that probably cost more than your average country parson’s annual salary. He carried a yellow silk handkerchief that, when pulled from his pocket, released a cloud of musk and bergamot so strong it made the entire theatre smell like a perfumery run by a duchess.

This, of course, was Mr. Dolphin—the great London manager, impresario, and theatrical titan. And beside him, like a loyal shadow, was his secretary, Mr. William Minns, who never left his side unless absolutely necessary—such as when he needed to go to the bathroom, which he did rarely and with great ceremony.

Within ten minutes of their arrival, the entire company had become aware of the visitor’s presence. They didn’t just act—they *performed*, as if auditioning for the throne of the dramatic arts.

Even Miss Fotheringay, whose heart was famously impervious to emotion, seemed to flutter slightly. Her part required little more than standing beautifully while holding a child (Master Talma Bingley, a toddler of unusually large proportions), but she executed it with such grace that one could almost believe she cared.

Still, the crowd remained silent. Pizarro received no applause. Bingley yelled. Mrs. Bingley roared. But Mr. Dolphin merely took snuff from a golden box the size of a shoe, sniffing with the solemnity of a judge.

Then came the final scene: Rolla staggers in with the infant, Cora rushes forward, shrieks, “O God, there’s blood upon him!”—and at last, the great man clapped.

“Bravo!” he thundered, slapping his secretary on the back. “By Jove, Billy, she’ll do!”

“Who taught her that dodge?” asked old Billy, a man whose sarcasm was as sharp as a dagger.

“I remember her at the Olympic,” he continued, “and I swear she couldn’t say ‘Bo’ to a goose.”

That, of course, was Mr. Bows, the orchestra’s little maestro, who had secretly coached her in the art of the dramatic cry. The entire company heard the applause. As the curtain fell, they swarmed around Cora, congratulating her—and also, quietly, hating her.

So how did Mr. Dolphin end up in this remote provincial theatre? Simple: he was broke.

Despite all his grandiose posters promising “Triumphs of Genius! Explosions of Talent! The Glories of English Drama!” his London theatre—the Museum Theatre, let us call it—was failing. The great Hubbard had played twenty nights of Shakespeare and failed to earn enough to buy a decent meal. The Cawdors, famed for their performances, drew crowds of exactly three people. Herr Garbage’s lions bit him. The Lyrical Drama, featuring Monsieur Poumons and an orchestra larger than a small army, nearly bankrupted Dolphin before it even opened.

So now he was scraping by on half-salaries, old comedies, and ballets performed by dancers who clearly hadn’t eaten in days.

Enter Lord Steyne—the Marquis of Steyne, noble patron of the arts, occasional statesman, and man who preferred to watch ballet from behind a curtain, like a king hiding from his own responsibilities.

He often attended the Museum Theatre, though usually late, arriving just in time for the dancing. He sat modestly behind a velvet screen, invisible to the audience, but unmistakable to the performers. Why? Because every dancer, every coryphée, every pirouetting nymph, would glance toward his box as if it contained the source of all beauty and power.

Once, during the Palm Dance in *Cook at Otaheite*, a hundred-twenty female savages in leafy costumes danced in perfect unison, all eyes fixed on the proscenium box. One misstep, and the whole thing would’ve collapsed. Yet Mademoiselle Sauterelle, mid-air, managed to wink at Lord Steyne before landing gracefully—like a cat who knows it’s being watched.

On this particular night, Steyne was surrounded by friends—Wenham, Wagg, and others—who were laughing so loudly that the pit demanded silence. “Shut up!” shouted a voice. “You’re ruining the atmosphere!”

But Steyne paid no mind. He was watching something else.

Wenham read aloud from a letter from Major Pendennis, who had been absent from London society for weeks—much to everyone’s dismay.

> “Dear Wenham,
> As you’ve had my character in your hands for the past three weeks and doubtless torn me to shreds, I think you can afford to be good-natured and help me. It’s a delicate matter, entre nous—une affaire de coeur. A young friend of mine is madly in love with Miss Fotheringay, actress at Chatteris. She’s as handsome as sin and better than any leading lady we’ve got. I want her in London. Can you get Dolphin to come see her? A word from a noble friend (you know what I mean) would be invaluable. If you can use Gaunt House influence—I promise anything in return. Do this for me, dear fellow. Yours truly, A. Pendennis.”

“Ah,” said Wenham, folding the letter. “Old Pendennis is in love.”

“And wants to get her out of Chatteris,” added Wagg. “I’d like to see him on his knees with rheumatism.”

“Or offering her a lock of his hair,” said Wagg again, grinning.

“Stuff,” said Steyne. “He has a nephew, doesn’t he? Probably the nephew’s affair. Young men get themselves into scrapes. I was once—when I was in the fifth form at Eton—a market-gardener’s daughter. Swore I’d marry her. I was mad about her—poor Polly!” He paused. For a moment, the boy George Gaunt seemed to rise from the past. “But she must be fine, from Pendennis’s description. Bring in Dolphin. Let’s see what he says.”

Wenham sprang from the box and hurried backstage. Dolphin was busy yelling at a ballerina for not keeping her toes pointed. “You’re supposed to be a star, not a lump of coal!” he roared.

But when Wenham appeared, the curses died instantly. Dolphin dropped his fist, bowed, and grasped the ambassador’s hand.

“How do, Mr. Wenham? How’s his lordship tonight? Looks uncommonly well,” he said, smoothing his wig like a man who’d just survived a crisis.

And so the visit to Chatteris was arranged. Dolphin wrote to Steyne, informing him that he had seen Miss Fotheringay, was “deeply impressed” by both her talent and appearance, and had already engaged her for London.

Pendennis read the announcement in the *Chatteris Chronicle*, where the editor praised her “unparalleled charm and consummate artistry,” predicting a brilliant career in the capital.

Bingley immediately began advertising: **“The Last Night of Miss Fotheringay’s Engagement!”**

Poor Pen and Sir Derby Oaks attended every performance. Sir Derby threw flowers like a romantic fool, receiving glances like a prize-winning dog. Pendennis, meanwhile, sat in the near-empty boxes, gaunt and hollow-eyed, as if he’d been abandoned by both love and sanity.

Only one other person seemed to care: Mr. Bows, the orchestra leader. One evening, he left his post, walked to Pendennis’s box, and offered his hand.

“Come walk with me,” he said.

They strolled down the street, then sat on Chatteris Bridge under the moonlight, talking about her.

“We may sit on the same bridge,” said Bows, “but we’ve been in the same boat for years. You’re not the only one who’s made a fool of himself over her. And I have less excuse—I’m older, I know her better. She has no heart. Not one. She’s colder than this stone you’re leaning on. She wouldn’t care if you drowned. Or if I did. Or if the bridge itself collapsed.”

“But she cares for you,” said Pendennis.

“She does,” admitted Bows, “because she needs me. I teach her. Without me, she’d be lost. She’ll send for me when she gets to London. She always does. But she won’t do it out of affection. Out of necessity.”

He flicked the end of his cigar into the river. It sparked, then vanished.

“And when she marries,” he continued, “she’ll throw me over—just like I’ll throw this cigar butt away.”

Pendennis rode home that night, thinking—not of himself, not of his broken heart—but of something else. Something bigger. Something… human.

And for the first time in weeks, he felt less alone.

Major Pendennis, ever the man of principle and strategy—especially when it came to keeping his nephew under wraps—decided that Fairoaks would remain under siege until the enemy had fully retreated. Which, in this case, meant simply that no one was allowed to leave the estate without a proper escort, or at least a suspiciously timed glance from an agent who looked like he’d been sent by the government to spy on your toothbrush.

Arthur Pendennis, the poor lad, wasn’t exactly being watched *like* a prisoner—no chains, no guard dogs, no midnight checks with a flashlight—but he might as well have been. His every movement was recorded, his footsteps tracked, and his thoughts likely filed under “Potential Risk: Romantic Inclinations.” If Arthur wanted to go fishing, someone was already packing a thermos of tea and a copy of *The Times* just in case.

Ah, love! What a fool’s errand. Who among us hasn’t been thwarted by the cruel hand of fate, the treachery of a woman (or worse, a man), or the sheer stupidity of our own choices? Let the reader pause, reflect, and remember the last time their heart was crushed by rejection—perhaps while trying to order coffee in a foreign country, or during a particularly awkward Tinder date. It’s not pretty. It’s not dignified. It’s usually accompanied by excessive sweating and a sudden interest in existential philosophy.

Now imagine Arthur Pendennis going through all that, but with a horse named Rebecca and a tendency to gallop into Chatteris screaming the name of the girl who rejected him—while also wearing a hat that probably cost more than most villagers’ annual incomes.

His mother, Mrs. Pendennis, was beside herself. Not because she understood the emotional turmoil her son was enduring—she didn’t. She was too busy worrying about whether he was eating enough bread and whether he’d grown taller since yesterday. But she loved him fiercely, which meant she suffered twice as much, even though she couldn’t quite explain why. Her idea of comfort was a hot cup of tea and a lecture on how boys should never wear hats indoors. Her idea of grief? Probably a long walk with a parasol and a muttered prayer.

Meanwhile, Major Pendennis, the man who could’ve been a general if he hadn’t chosen to live in a country house and pretend he was still relevant, was doing his best to pass the time. He read newspapers with the intensity of a man decoding ancient scrolls. He played backgammon with the widow, who tried her hardest to make it fun, but really only succeeded in making him feel guilty for winning. He dressed every day like he was attending a royal ball—even though the only guest he ever saw was a very polite badger.

And then there was the American war. Oh yes, the Major had recently discovered a deep passion for the American war. Or rather, two volumes of the *Annual Register* that happened to be in his bedroom. He studied them religiously, muttering things like “Saint Lucie was a hero!” and “Wyoming was tragic!”—as if he’d personally fought in both battles. It was impressive, really. A man so bored he could invent a new hobby based solely on secondhand history.

Arthur, meanwhile, found solace in the carp pond at Clavering Park. A murky, whispering pool surrounded by reeds, green alders, and the ghost of a milkmaid who drowned herself centuries ago. (She was said to haunt the place, but mostly just stared sadly at people fishing and sighed.) Arthur didn’t drown himself—he merely fished. And wrote poetry. Terrible poetry. Poems so bad they made you wonder if he’d been using a different kind of bait—perhaps despair?

He once composed a sonnet titled *Ode to My Unrequited Love*, which began:

> "Thou art the moon, my soul’s lone star,
> Whose light I seek, yet cannot bear…"

And ended with something about "the wind" and "a broken lute." Honestly, the fish were probably laughing.

But it wasn’t all sorrow and anguished verses. Arthur had moments of wild energy. When he felt particularly low, he’d saddle Rebecca and ride off into the countryside, screaming the name of the actress who broke his heart. Carters would jump out of their way, turnpike men would drop their tollbooks, and sheep would scatter like they’d seen a dragon. One time, he rode past the Clavering Arms and shouted, “Rebecca! You’ve left me for a man with *baldness*!”—which is probably not what the actress was expecting.

Then there was Mr. Harry Foker, the young lord of the land who arrived on a tandem—yes, a *tandem*—and caused a sensation in Clavering market. He upset a stall selling pickled onions, accidentally cut Mrs. Pybus’s poodle across the rear end (which, according to rumor, was the dog’s favorite spot), and drank raspberry bitters like it was water. The villagers were baffled. How could such a young man have such a title? Why did he wear a waistcoat with buttons shaped like tiny horses? And why did he bring a *dog* named Stoopid?

Foker became a regular visitor, and everyone loved him—except the widow and little Laura, who thought he was slightly unhinged. But then again, who wasn’t slightly unhinged in Clavering?

Clavering itself was a charming little town—when viewed from afar. From the London road, it looked like a painting: a winding river, old trees, a church tower peeking above rooftops, and hills rolling gently toward the sea. Tom Smith, the coachman, used to point to a tree near the river and say, “Artises come and take hoff the Church from that there tree—it was a Habby once, sir.” (Which, naturally, confused everyone.)

But up close? Not so much. On market days, it was lively. On other days, it was quiet enough to hear the creak of the ensign at the Clavering Arms. There hadn’t been a ball in the Assembly Rooms since the old Sir Francis Clavering died, and the stables—once home to a regiment of cavalry—were now occupied by farmers’ carts and the occasional goat.

The church, however, was grand. Towering grey stones, carved windows, and effigies of dead baronets kneeling in eternal prayer. One statue showed Dame Rebecca Clavering being carried to heaven by two blue-veined angels who looked like they were barely holding on. Pen used to stare at them during sermons, wondering if they were tired of the job.

The congregation was decent—mostly servants, schoolchildren, and the occasional apothecary. Wapshot’s boys made noise. Mrs. Pybus alternated between churches. And the Misses Finucane’s girls wore bonnets that seemed to say, “We are here to learn, but also to look presentable.”

But the real drama was happening in the *other* church—the New Church, led by Mr. Simcoe and his wife, the Honourable Mrs. Simcoe. They were modern, evangelical, and absolutely *not* impressed by Doctor Portman’s taste for whist and the theater. Their tracts fluttered into every door like angry birds, and soon, half the town had abandoned the old church for the new one.

Mrs. Portman, bless her, was the chief gossip of Clavering. She knew everything—what the butcher bought, what the milliner sold, and especially what Arthur Pendennis had done in Chatteris. Once she heard the news—about the actress, the proposal, the whole romantic disaster—she couldn’t keep quiet. By the next morning, it was all over town. At the bookshop, the milliner’s, the shoe store, the factory—everyone was talking.

“Gracious goodness,” the ladies whispered, “how infatuated the mother is! That boy thinks he’s a lord just because he rides a horse!”

And indeed, Arthur *did* give himself airs. He didn’t wear corduroys. He didn’t play cricket. He didn’t care for village life. He preferred riding Rebecca, reading novels, and composing poetry that made the cows groan.

So when the scandal broke—when the story of the actress reached Clavering—it was like dropping a lit match into a pile of dry hay. The village erupted.

Wapshot’s boys mocked him. The factory workers jeered. Someone chalked “Fotheringay for ever!” on the gate of Fairoaks. Another brought a playbill from Chatteris and taped it to the wall like a declaration of war.

Then came the incident in the churchyard.

Arthur, riding through Lower Town, heard laughter. He turned into the churchyard—where Wapshot’s boys were lounging. One of them, Hobnell Major (a name that sounds like a villain in a Dickens novel), struck a theatrical pose near a fresh grave and began reciting Hamlet’s speech over Ophelia—with a smirk aimed directly at Arthur.

Arthur, who had spent years suppressing his temper, finally snapped. He charged forward, whipped out his riding crop, and slashed Hobnell across the face. Then he threw the whip aside, roared something unprintable, and punched Hobnell straight into the grave—just as it was waiting for its rightful occupant.

The boys scattered. Doctor Portman arrived, gasping. Arthur stood there, fists clenched, eyes blazing, daring anyone else to step forward. No one did.

Later, Arthur went to Baymouth to consult Harry Foker. Foker, ever the gentleman, sent Stoopid (his dog) with a note to Hobnell. The note read: “Dear Mr. Hobnell, I hope you’re feeling better. I’d like to discuss the matter. Please let me know when you’re available.”

Stoopid returned with word that the note had been read aloud to six boys, who laughed and decided Hobnell would reply “arter arternoon school.” And that Mr. Wapshot would come out in his Master’s gown—which, as Stoopid knew, meant he was ready for a fight.

Thus ended Chapter XV, where love, honor, and a single poorly timed insult led to a brawl in a graveyard, a dog named Stoopid, and the birth of a legend that would be told in Clavering for generations.

And if you ask me, it’s the kind of story that deserves a sequel. Maybe with more ponies.

Pen’s little scandal—what a storm it stirred up! The whole village of Clavering seemed to be buzzing like a hive of bees that had just discovered a particularly juicy bit of gossip. And poor Pen, who’d merely defended his honor (or so he claimed) by throwing a punch at Foker after some insult involving Miss Costigan, now found himself the center of a social hurricane.

Doctor Portman, ever the model of moral rectitude and stiff upper lip, was furious. “You’ve disgraced yourself!” he thundered, pacing back and forth like a lion in a cage. “Violence? Threatening duels? You’re not a knight of old, you’re a boy with a bad haircut and worse manners!”

Pen, meanwhile, took this all with the solemn gravity of a man who’d just been appointed Prime Minister. He sat upright in his chair, chin raised, eyes wide with the seriousness of being *wronged*. “I regret the affair,” he announced gravely, “but I cannot allow myself to be insulted without defending my honor. A gentleman must do what is required.”

He then turned to his uncle, Major Pendennis, and asked with great dignity: “Uncle, could I have acted otherwise?”

“Vous allez trop vite, my good sir,” replied the Major, scratching his head as if trying to untangle a knot in his own thoughts. “Between gentlemen, perhaps. But between two schoolboys? It’s absurd. Like a pair of sparrows squabbling over a crumb.”

Pen blinked. “But it was an outrage!”

“Yes, yes,” said the Major, “but you’re not in the army yet. No pistols. No bloodshed. Just go back to your Latin.”

Mrs. Pendennis, on the other hand, was practically fainting into her handkerchief. “Oh, Arthur!” she wailed. “You’ve done something terrible! You’ve brought shame upon us all! That horrid Foker—why did he have to be so wicked? Why couldn’t he just leave you alone?!”

Pen kissed her forehead with theatrical tenderness. “Women don’t understand these things, dear mother. I placed myself in Foker’s hands. It was the only course.”

The Major grinned. “Ah, yes. The noble code of honor. How very grown-up.” Then he added sotto voce, “Though I suspect it was more about pride than principle.”

And so the family was divided: the doctor scolding, the mother weeping, the uncle chuckling, and Pen pretending he was the hero of a melodrama.

Still, despite the drama, one thing was certain: Pen was going to college. October term, no ifs or buts. Foker had promised to introduce him to “the right set”—a phrase that sounded suspiciously like “the people who drink too much and get into fights.” The Major, however, saw it as a golden opportunity. “Mr. Foker knows the very best young men at the University,” he declared. “There’s the young Marquis of Plinlimmon, Lord Magnus Charters, and even Lady Agnes will invite him to Logwood!”

Helen sighed. “I suppose you know best, brother.”

She didn’t believe it for a second. She knew Mr. Foker was a charming rogue—a man who could charm birds from trees and ladies from their husbands. But she also knew he was trouble wrapped in a waistcoat and polished with a smile.

Still, the decision was made. Pen would go. And as for Smirke, well… let’s just say he wasn’t exactly thrilled.

Smirke, the poor curate, had become Pen’s emotional confidante. With no one else to talk to—his mother wouldn’t listen, his uncle thought love was a distraction, and Foker was far too busy plotting how to steal another man’s horse—Pen poured his heart out to Smirke like a river flooding its banks.

He wrote sonnets. Dozens of them. In English, in Latin, in Greek (well, mostly in English). He dedicated them to “Amaryllis,” which, of course, was just a fancy name for Miss Costigan. He recited them aloud, often in the shrubbery, where the squirrels probably nodded in approval.

Smirke, bless him, pretended to be deeply moved. He praised every line. He said they were “exquisite” and “profound.” He even told Pen that one particular Sapphic verse reminded him of the beauty of nature—though really, it just rhymed “moon” with “soon,” which is hardly Shakespearean.

Pen, naturally, was delighted. He gave Smirke his best books, his gold chain, and even tried to give him his double-barrelled gun—though the Major stopped that before it could end in disaster. Pen also bought a gold pencil-case on credit (he was broke, remember) and inscribed it: “To my dearest friend, whose wisdom and kindness are beyond measure. Always yours, Arthur.”

Smirke accepted it with trembling hands and a heart full of emotion—and possibly a tiny bit of guilt.

But now came the cruel blow: Pen was leaving. College loomed. And with it, the end of Smirke’s daily visits to Fairoaks. The house where he’d been welcomed, where he’d seen Mrs. Pendennis smile, where he’d once dared to hope…

He couldn’t bear it. What would he do? Where would he go? Who would he talk to about his feelings?

He began to wonder: should he confess his love? Should he kneel before the widow and beg for her hand? Or should he wait? Because if he confessed and she refused… well, that would be the end of everything. The gate of Fairoaks would shut forever. And inside that gate lay his entire world.

Meanwhile, Madame Fribsby—the milliner with the French accent and the heart of a romance novelist—had taken matters into her own hands. She believed, with absolute certainty, that Smirke was in love with Mrs. Pendennis. And since she herself was a hopeless romantic (and slightly delusional), she decided to play matchmaker.

Every time she saw Mrs. Pendennis, she would gush: “Oh, such a gentle soul! Such a lonely man! Why, no lady would be so kind as to take pity on him!”

Mrs. Pendennis, of course, laughed politely and changed the subject. But Madame Fribsby was relentless. She spoke in French, half-remembered phrases from novels, and occasionally whispered, “Mong cure a boco souffare,” which roughly translated to “My heart is breaking.”

Smirke, meanwhile, was becoming increasingly nervous. Every time Madame Fribsby praised him, he blushed. Every time she mentioned Mrs. Pendennis, he sighed. And when she said, “Je conny cela biang,” meaning “I know this well,” he nearly fainted.

It was all too much. And then came the moment of truth: Major Pendennis informed Smirke that his services were no longer needed. “We’re grateful, sir,” he said grandly, “but the young gentleman is off to Oxford. You may retire to your parsonage with our warmest thanks.”

Smirke stood there, stunned. His life was ending. His dreams were crumbling. And Madame Fribsby, sensing the crisis, immediately ran to tell Mrs. Pybus.

“Oh, my dear!” she cried. “I know everything! There’s a secret attachment! A marriage is imminent!”

And thus, the gossip machine roared to life again.

Mrs. Pybus, Mrs. Speers, Mrs. Wapshot—they all gathered in Madame Fribsby’s shop, sipping tea and whispering like conspirators. “It’s all settled,” said Mrs. Pybus. “The boy goes to college, and then the widow marries the curate.”

“She’s been seeing him every day,” said Mrs. Speers.

“Poor Mr. Pendennis would turn in his grave,” said Mrs. Wapshot.

“And she married him for his money,” added Mrs. Pybus, “just like a common harlot.”

“It’s rather too open,” said someone else, “to pretend it’s just a tutor.”

Then, as if summoned by fate, Mrs. Portman entered the shop—her face pale, her hat tilted just so, her voice dropping to a whisper: “I heard it all.”

She rushed home and told Doctor Portman, who exploded like a volcano.

“That milksop of a curate!” he roared. “Pretending to be worthy of Mrs. Pendennis? Why, he’s barely out of his teens! And she’s old enough to be his mother!”

“She might be,” said Mrs. Portman, “but she’s still a woman. And he’s a low-church fool who thinks prayer is just a formality.”

So the curate was cast aside, though not entirely. For while the Doctor wanted to fire him, he couldn’t bring himself to do it—because, deep down, he knew Smirke was harmless. And maybe even a little sweet.

Back at Fairoaks, Pen continued to brood. He’d lost his beloved Amaryllis, his tutor, and now, it seemed, his reputation. Rumors flew: that he’d hanged himself in the orchard; that Miss Costigan had jilted him; that he’d paid her off with a thousand pounds (though no one could agree on the exact amount).

When he heard these stories, Pen went wild. “Why isn’t Pybus a man?” he yelled. “I’d challenge her to a duel!”

He glared at Simcoe, the local clergyman, so fiercely that Simcoe went home and wrote a sermon titled “The Perils of Youth and the Dangers of Theatrical Entertainment,” clearly aimed at Pen.

And so, like Achilles in his tent, Pen sulked. He refused to speak. He ate nothing. He stared out the window, imagining the glory of college life—where he would finally escape the gossip, the scandals, and the unbearable weight of being loved by a milliner, adored by a curate, and hated by everyone else.

But even in his anger, he couldn’t help wondering: Was it worth it? To leave behind the only person who truly listened to him?

Perhaps, he thought, it was. Perhaps the world was ready for Arthur Pendennis.

Or perhaps it wasn’t.

Either way, the next chapter was already writing itself.

The Curate—Mr. Smirke, a man whose soul was as delicate as a wet handkerchief and whose heart beat like a clockwork mouse—had just finished his daily pilgrimage to Fairoaks, where he was currently pretending to teach Arthur Pendennis Latin while secretly composing sonnets about someone who wasn’t even in the room. It was during this noble act of scholarly pretense that Mrs. Portman, having completed her business with Mrs. Pybus (a woman whose gossip could curdle milk at fifty paces), decided that the weather was so gloriously fine it would be a crime not to visit her dear friend at Fairoaks.

She arrived just in time to drop a bombshell—or rather, a perfectly timed secret—into the middle of tea.

“Now,” said Mrs. Portman, sipping her tea with the air of one who knew exactly what she was doing, “I’ve been meaning to tell you something about Mr. Smirke.”

“Oh?” said Mrs. Pendennis, raising an eyebrow like a finely tuned instrument. “And what is it? Has he finally confessed to stealing my silver?”

“No, no,” Mrs. Portman replied, “much more serious. He’s engaged. To a lady. Miss Thompson of Clapham Common. Been engaged for years, poor dear.”

“Engaged?” cried Mrs. Pendennis, nearly spilling her tea. “But he’s only just arrived! And I thought—well, I thought he was looking elsewhere!”

“Ah,” said Mrs. Portman, leaning forward conspiratorially, “that’s precisely why I’m telling you. Because people have been saying he’s smitten with *you*, Helen.”

“Me?” shrieked Mrs. Pendennis, turning as red as a beetroot. “Why, that’s absurd! He’s my son’s tutor! A gentleman of modest means and even more modest manners! Why, I wouldn’t marry him if he were made of gold and wore a crown!”

“Well,” said Mrs. Portman, “that’s good. Because now we know he’s not marrying you. So all’s well.”

Major Pendennis, who had been reading the newspaper with the intensity of a man trying to decipher ancient runes, put it down slowly. “Hmph,” he said. “This is getting out of hand. That fellow has been here long enough to learn how to behave like a gentleman—but never quite enough to become one.”

“I always thought he was too kind,” said Helen, feeling a sudden pang of guilt. “Too polite. Too helpful. He brought books. He copied music. He once gave Laura a toy horse made entirely of wood and hope. And yet I treated him like a servant. A servant with glasses.”

“Exactly,” said the Major. “You mustn’t be too kind to tutors. They begin to think they’re family. Then they start asking questions. Then they start making eyes at your sister-in-law. Then—”

“Then nothing!” snapped Helen. “He’s going to marry Miss Thompson. That’s settled.”

“Is it?” asked the Major, raising an eyebrow. “Because I’ve seen him look at you like a man who’s lost his way in a forest and found a map written in tears.”

Helen blushed furiously. She did not deny it. But she also didn’t say anything. Instead, when Mr. Smirke came downstairs from the study (looking slightly dazed, as if he’d just read a particularly emotional sermon), she marched up to him with surprising grace and shook his hand.

“Mr. Smirke,” she said, “I’ve been unkind. I’ve been rude. I’ve been… well, I’ve been me. Please come into the drawing-room. We rarely see you there.”

Smirke, whose heart was already fluttering like a trapped bird, nearly dropped his hat.

“And dinner,” continued Helen, “will be served soon. You’re welcome to join us.”

Smirke, who had spent the past two years imagining dinner with her as a dream sequence involving candlelight and angels, accepted with trembling hands.

At dinner, things began to unravel—mostly because of the wine.

Pen, who had recently been granted the sacred keys to the cellar (a privilege usually reserved for those who hadn’t yet discovered the meaning of moderation), had brought out a bottle of claret so old it had probably witnessed the fall of Rome. Or at least a very dramatic argument between two butlers.

As the elders left the table, Pen and Smirke were left alone—with a second bottle, a third, and a growing sense of destiny.

Pen, now flushed and poetic, began reciting verses he had composed earlier that morning—poems about love, betrayal, and the crushing weight of unrequited affection. The verses were melodramatic, heartfelt, and utterly ridiculous.

> “The woman who rejected my passion,” he declaimed, “cannot be worthy to win it.
> I am waking from love’s mad fever—yes, I am—
> And though my heart may never be whole again,
> Though death itself seems sweet compared to life,
> Yet I shall rise—like a phoenix—like a poet—
> Like a man who will one day be famous—*and* respected!”

Smirke, who had been quietly sipping his wine, suddenly leaned forward, eyes wide.

“You are a poet,” he said, voice cracking. “A great poet. One day, you’ll be celebrated. And when you are, will you remember your old tutor? Your friend?”

Pen grabbed his hand across the apricot tarts. “Of course I will! You’ve been my best friend. My only true friend. God bless you, old boy.”

Smirke, now sweating like a man who’d just run a marathon through a thunderstorm, whispered, “Am I… really?”

“Yes,” said Pen. “You’re my best friend. My only true friend. Now, let’s drink to your fiancée.”

Smirke froze.

“To whom?” he asked.

“To Miss Thompson,” said Pen, laughing. “Or is it someone else? Someone here? Not old Pybus? Not Miss Rolt? She’s only fourteen!”

“It’s not Miss Portman?” Pen added, grinning. “She’s got a *devilish* large waist. But I wish you joy anyway.”

Smirke groaned. “It’s not Miss Portman,” he said, voice breaking. “It’s someone older than me. Someone… here.”

Pen burst out laughing. “Madame Fribsby? By Jove, it’s Madame Fribsby! The immortal Madame Frib!”

Smirke could take no more. He threw his head back and cried, “No! Not Madame Fribsby! I’ve loved someone else for two years—someone pure, someone angelic—someone whose beauty transcends mere human form!”

Pen stopped laughing. “Who?”

“The most perfect woman in the world,” said Smirke, trembling. “Your mother.”

Pen jumped up. “My mother? You’re mad! She’s seven or eight years older than you!”

“Did you find that objectionable?” Smirke asked, voice rising.

Pen blushed. “Well… no. But still. It’s not right. You can’t propose to a lady of such rank! It’s a breach of confidence! A liberty!”

Smirke clutched his hands together. “But I love her! I’ve loved her since the moment I saw her—her kindness, her grace, her ability to make tea without burning the kettle!”

Pen stared. “You mean… you’re in love with my mother?”

“Yes,” said Smirke, sobbing. “And I wanted to ask her to marry me. But I couldn’t. I didn’t know how. I was afraid.”

Pen looked at him, then at the empty bottles, then at the ceiling. “So you’ve been mooning over my mother—while I’ve been mooning over Miss Costigan, who’s probably married by now and living in a cottage with six cats?”

He paused. “And you think I should care about this?”

“No,” said Smirke, “but I hoped you’d understand.”

Pen sighed. “Understand? Of course I understand. But I can’t let you marry my mother. It’s not proper. It’s not right. It’s not—well, it’s just not.”

He rang the bell. “Bring coffee. And get Mr. Smirke’s horse ready. He’s leaving.”

Smirke, heartbroken, nodded silently. He put on his coat, bowed to Helen (who was still walking the lawn like a queen contemplating a tragic fate), and walked out to the stable.

Pen followed, still half-drunk, half-awake, half-horrified. As Smirke mounted his pony, Pen held out his hand.

“Forgive me,” he said. “If I said anything harsh. You’ve been very kind.”

Smirke nodded. “I’ll always remember you, Arthur.”

Pen watched him ride away, disappearing down the road like a ghost fleeing from its own heartbreak.

When he returned to his mother, he grinned. “Smirke’s unwell,” he said.

Helen smiled. “Oh, dear. Poor man.”

They walked together, arm in arm, under the setting sun. Laura tapped on the window, laughing. The Major called from inside: “Your coffee’s cold!”

That night, Pen told his mother everything—how Smirke had confessed, how he’d been drunk, how he’d almost proposed to her.

Helen laughed—then blushed—then laughed again.

“Confound the man’s impudence,” said the Major later. “What next? Will he try to marry the parson’s cat?”

But deep down, he was amused. And so was everyone else.

Meanwhile, Captain Costigan, having paid off all his debts (with money from Mr. Dolphin), was given a farewell banquet by the Buccaneers—a club of gentlemen who believed in three things: punch, loyalty, and drinking until they forgot their names.

The toast was raised. The speech was given. Captain Costigan, moved beyond words, declared Chatteris the most beautiful city in England—“though I must confess,” he said, “the ladies of London are somewhat taller.”

And so, with a final cheer, he left—his daughter’s future secured, his honor restored, and his liver forever grateful.

And thus ends the first part of this history—where love, wine, and miscommunication reign supreme.

Every man, however brief or inglorious his time at college may have been, carries in his heart a soft spot for those old university days—those golden hours when the world seemed wide and full of promise, and your only worry was whether you’d get caught sneaking out after curfew. The young man’s life is just beginning: the boy’s leash has been cut, and he’s free to explore the dizzy delights of liberty. He hasn’t yet learned about bills, bad colds, betrayal, or the crushing disappointment of Monday morning. The play hasn’t been performed so many times that it’s lost its charm. Even if the after-drink—repeated like a mechanical prayer—is now flat and bitter, remember how pure and sparkling that first sip of pleasure tasted! How eagerly the lad lunges for the cup, draining it with wild abandon!

Now, let’s be honest: there’s nothing quite as satisfying as watching someone else enjoy themselves—especially when you’re stuck with a poached egg and a glass of water. It’s like seeing your kids laugh at a pantomime when you can’t go yourself. So here’s hoping no one ever grows so jaded by age or experience that they stop smiling at the sight of happy youth.

A few weeks back, I returned from a short visit to the venerable University of Oxbridge—home, once upon a time, to our dear friend Mr. Arthur Pendennis. I took the train, sharing a compartment with a young student from Saint Boniface College. He’d somehow managed to secure an exeat (a rare feat, these days), and was determined to make the most of a day off in London. From the moment we boarded, he didn’t stop talking—jokes, laughter, stories about the latest scandal in the dining hall, the state of the rowing team, and why the porter at the common room wasn’t nearly as good as it used to be. Honestly, I could’ve listened all day. But the journey ended far too soon.

At the station, nothing would do but a hansom cab—he wanted to race into town and dive headfirst into the pleasures awaiting him. Off he went, grinning like a madman, joy lighting up his face like a candle in a storm. Meanwhile, I, humble servant of the reader, had only a small carpet-bag, so I climbed onto the roof of the omnibus, where I sat contentedly between a Jewish peddler smoking cheap cigars and a gentleman’s valet guarding a poodle who looked like it had seen better days. We waited patiently until we were full to bursting—passengers, boxes, and one very disgruntled dog—and then the coachman drove off at a leisurely pace. No hurry. No rush. Neither of us was itching to arrive in this smoky Babylon of London, nor did we care much about dinner at the club or dancing at the Casino. But give it a few years, and my young friend will be just as indifferent—perhaps even more so.

Back in Arthur Pendennis’s day, there were no trains. He arrived at Oxbridge in a grand coach, packed inside and out with dons, gownsmen, fresh-faced undergraduates, and their overprotective guardians. One particularly nervous gentleman from the City—fat, pale, wearing grey stockings—sat next to Major Pendennis, while his son sat across from him, looking like he’d rather be anywhere else. When they heard the coach had been driven for two stages by young Mr. Foker of Saint Boniface College—who, according to rumor, could drive as well as Tom Hicks himself—the father turned green with fear.

Arthur, meanwhile, perched on the roof, gazing down with wide-eyed curiosity. He studied the coach, the passengers, the passing countryside—all of it thrilling. Then, suddenly, the great towers and spires of Oxbridge came into view: ancient buildings, towering elms, and the shimmering river beyond. His heart leapt. This was it. The real thing.

Before setting off, Arthur had spent a few days at his uncle’s lodgings in Bury Street. Major Pendennis thought the lad needed new clothes—nothing unusual, really, since Arthur was already fond of anything that involved shiny buttons or fancy waistcoats. The uncle spared no expense, though he probably wouldn’t have admitted it. London, during this visit, felt eerily quiet. Pall Mall was deserted; the red jackets had all fled to the country. The clubs’ bow-windows were dark. It was like visiting a ghost town.

Still, the Major took his nephew to see a few of these abandoned mansions, and even got Arthur’s name added to the candidate list of one of them. “Arthur Pendennis, Esquire, of Fairoaks Lodge, ——shire, and Saint Boniface College, Oxbridge,” read the parchment, “proposed by Major Pendennis, seconded by Viscount Colchicum.” Arthur read it aloud, trembling with pride. “You’ll be eligible for ballot in three years—by which time you’ll have taken your degree,” the Major said, chuckling. Arthur couldn’t wait. He already imagined himself strolling through the stucco halls, lounging in the library, hosting parties in the drawing-room. The Major watched him with amusement, shaking his head at the young man’s pompous strut as he left the building.

Later, Arthur and Foker took a cab to the Grey Friars School, where they ran into old friends. Kids swarmed around the cab, admiring the chestnut horse, the livery, and Stoopid the tiger—Foker’s personal attendant, who looked like he’d been carved from oak and painted black. The bell for afternoon school rang just as they were chatting. The dreaded Doctor emerged with his Latin grammar clutched in hand. Foker slunk away, but Arthur stepped forward, blushing, and shook the man’s hand. He remembered how that same grammar had once slapped him across the face for misplacing an accent. “Good old book,” he muttered to himself. “We had some battles.”

They then headed to Foker’s brewery—a massive complex not far from the school, where signs bearing the name “Foker’s Entire” glittered everywhere. The junior partner, a dignified old fellow, welcomed them with silver flagons of brown stout so strong it seemed to affect even the horse. Harry Foker’s steed galloped home at breakneck speed, narrowly missing pie stalls, pedestrians, and street posts. Stoopid swung wildly behind, clinging to the board like a man trying to survive a hurricane.

The Major enjoyed every minute of it. He listened intently to Foker’s rambling tales, treated both boys to a fine dinner at a Covent Garden coffeehouse, and later escorted them to the theater. But the highlight? A dinner invitation from Mr. and Lady Agnes Foker, who happened to be in town. “Having secured entry into Lady Agnes’s house,” the Major told Arthur solemnly, “it is your duty to preserve it. Always call when you’re in London. Read Debrett carefully—look up the Roshervilles. Make a few polite references to their history. You’ve got a poetic mind—use it. And never refuse the family porter after dinner. Even if it disagrees with you, like it does me—confoundedly.”

And true to his word, the Major drank the porter—though he grimaced like a man being forced to swallow vinegar.

Lady Agnes, wrapped in maternal devotion, greeted Arthur warmly. She raved about her son’s rigorous studies, fearing they might harm his delicate health. The elder Foker burst out laughing at her melodrama. Arthur winked at his friend. Then Lady Agnes launched into a detailed recounting of Harry’s childhood: his miraculous recovery from measles, his near-drowning incident, the cruelty of the schoolmaster (whom she refused to forgive), and so on. After an hour of this, she declared the Pendennises delightful men and offered them pheasants from Logwood—her estate—inviting them to visit during Christmas or vacation.

That night, as they lit candles in Bury Street, the Major whispered to Arthur: “You handled the Rosherville reference nicely—though you shouldn’t blush so. Remember: with the right introduction, good society is just as easy to access as bad. It takes no more effort to dine with nobility than with a lawyer in Bedford Square. Choose your friends wisely. Your first steps matter most. Did you write to your mother today? No? Do it before you leave. Ask Foker for a frank—they appreciate it. Goodnight. God bless you.”

Arthur wrote a hilarious letter home—full of jokes about the play, the school, the brewery, and the Fokers’ dinner. His mother, praying in her lonely house at Fairoaks, read it with tears in her eyes. She and Laura read it again and again, cherishing every word. This was Arthur’s first real step into the world. Ah, what a perilous journey it is—how easily the bravest stumble, the strongest fall. Brother traveler, may you find a kind arm to guide you, a helping hand for those who trip. May truth light your way, mercy forgive your faults, and love walk beside you always. Without that light, how blind the path, how dark the road.

Finally, the coach rolled into the Trencher Inn in Main Street, Oxbridge. Arthur, thrilled, noticed gownsmen walking past, chapel bells ringing, towers rising above rooftops. He had arrived.

Dr. Portman had already spoken with Mr. Buck, Tutor of Saint Boniface, on Arthur’s behalf. Once the Major ensured Arthur looked presentable—no one wants a freshman arriving in torn trousers—they walked down Main Street, passed Saint George’s College (the grandest in Oxbridge), and arrived at Saint Boniface. As they entered through the ivy-covered gate, Arthur’s heart beat faster. Above the entrance stood a dome draped in vines, and a statue of Saint Boniface, arms raised in blessing. Coats of arms adorned the walls—royal, noble, proud.

The porter pointed to a quirky tower where Mr. Buck lived. They crossed the quadrangle—Arthur absorbing every detail: the fountain, the chapel windows, the hall with its lantern, the Master emerging in rustling silks, the chimneys, turrets, and gables. The scene was etched into his memory forever.

Saint George’s was huge—four quadrangles, magnificent gardens, and students who wore gowns with airs of superiority. Saint Boniface, by comparison, was modest. But it had a fine reputation. Its families were prestigious, its livings generous, its fellowships easy, its boat third on the river, its choir excellent, and its ale—oh, its ale was the best in Oxbridge.

Inside the wainscoted hall, beneath Roubilliac’s statue of Saint Boniface (blessing the table with divine approval), hung portraits of famous alumni: Doctor Griddle (burnt under Henry VIII), Archbishop Bush (who roasted him), Lord Chief Justice Hicks, the Duke of St. David’s, Sprott the Poet, Doctor Blogg (friend of Johnson), and others. The cook was legendary—his son had topped Camford. The wine in the fellows’ room was renowned.

Arthur entered this cozy haven, leaning on his uncle’s arm. They reached Mr. Buck’s rooms. The tutor had received glowing reports from Dr. Portman: “A young gentleman of fortune, ancient lineage, and such character and genius that, with proper guidance, he will be a credit to the college.” Naturally, Mr. Buck was delighted. He invited the Major to dine in hall, offered Arthur the best rooms available—once occupied by a gentleman-pensioner named Spicer—and suggested they take wine afterward.

After parting ways, they returned to the ante-room, where a servant waited with a bag full of caps and gowns. Arthur tried on a gown—it fit perfectly. Then came the cap, which he placed jauntily to one side, just like Fiddicombe, the youngest master at Grey Friars. He admired himself in the gilt mirror, adjusting the cap with flair. The Major smiled. He liked the look of the lad.

Then Davis, the skip, led them across the quadrangle to the rooms—spacious, high-beamed, with deep-set windows. Mr. Spicer’s furniture remained, to be sold. The Major agreed to buy what was useful, politely declining six sporting prints and four opera dancers in gauze draperies—“No thank you,” he said, laughing. “Not my taste.”

They went to hall. Arthur sat with other freshmen, eating his commons. The Major joined the high table with tutors and fathers. Afterward, they drank wine with Mr. Buck, then attended chapel. The Major sat gravely in the upper seat, watching the Master—Doctor Donne—in his carved stall beneath the organ loft, holding a massive prayer book like a sacred relic. All the freshmen behaved well—except Foker, who arrived late and giggled with his cronies like they were at a music hall.

Ah, those were different times—William IV was king. Young men are better behaved now. Still, Saint Boniface had a reputation for being… *fast*.

That night, Arthur couldn’t sleep. He tossed and turned, eager to begin his college life. What was he thinking about? His mother? Yes—poor soul, whose whole life revolved around him. Miss Fotheringay? Oh yes—his eternal passion, the source of so many sleepless nights, so much longing. He blushed, muttering incoherent exclamations—“How could I have loved such a woman? How could I have stooped so low?” He laughed bitterly. “Poor old Smirke! I’ll write and cheer him up. He won’t die of it—ha, ha!”

If the Major had been awake, he might have heard a dozen such outbursts. Perhaps it would’ve been better for Arthur to have spent the night in quiet reflection. But the world had already caught hold of him—through his selfish mentor, his uncle. And anyone who’s followed Arthur’s story knows: he was weak, impulsive, vain, generous, selfish, fickle—exactly the sort of young man who thrives on self-gratification.

Six months of passionate heartbreak had aged him. There was a vast difference between the innocent boy of eighteen and the man who now saw love clearly—for what it truly was. Arthur had gained experience, confidence, and a certain arrogance. And now, as he began his life among his peers, he carried with him not just books and linen, but a new Alma Mater—one he’d have to learn to love—or flee from.

When Major Pendennis bid farewell to his nephew Pen on the second day after their arrival at Oxbridge, it’s safe to say that neither man was particularly sorry to see the other go—though for very different reasons. The Major, having spent three months of what he considered precious leisure time tutoring a young man who clearly didn’t need much tutoring, was thrilled to be free of the burden. He’d sacrificed his peace, his privacy, and possibly even his sanity for the sake of “education.” Let us pause a moment and consider: how many men—especially Majors—would willingly surrender such a stretch of life to mentor a relative? A man may lay down his life for honor, but ask him to give up his pipe, his nap, or his favorite armchair, and you’ll see a different kind of resistance. Few can endure that sort of sacrifice. Now, if you happen to have a beard (and let’s face it, many of you do), would you have done the same? I won’t claim women wouldn’t—after all, they’re trained from childhood to put others first. But men? We’re not exactly known for our self-denial. Ask yourself: how many truly saintly men do you know? Ten? Try five. And if you find ten, send me a note—I’ll bring wine.

Anyway, the Major left in high spirits, waving from the coach as Foker and Pen waved back. Foker made sure the coachman knew to treat the Major with special care—“He’s an important gentleman,” he said solemnly, as if the Major were a visiting prince rather than a slightly flustered military man. The Major dashed off to London, then to Cheltenham, where he promptly descended upon several nearby country estates like a hawk spotting pigeons. Shooting, good company, and no lectures—this was the life.

Now, while some authors might spend half a novel chronicling one week of a character’s college career, we shall not. Pen’s academic journey is too rich to cram into endless detail, unless some future historian decides to pick up the pen after my demise and write a sequel for your grandchildren. But let’s be honest: most boys’ college lives are not exactly gripping reading. Yours? Mine? Not really. As long as we keep our honor intact, we’re content. Women remain pure. Men? Well, let’s just say they don’t. Poor Arthur Pendennis wasn’t worse than the rest—he was just part of the pack. And the pack, dear reader, is generally not a model of virtue. Can you name ten spotless men? I can’t. My list includes a few decent fellows, but saints? Hardly.

Pen began his studies with admirable diligence. He attended lectures in classics and mathematics, though he soon realized he had little talent for either. Worse still, he discovered that two particularly unrefined young men—men whose trousers lacked straps, whose shoes were thick and coarse, and whose socks looked like they’d been knitted by a badger—were outperforming him. This was unacceptable. So Pen dropped math and classical science entirely and announced to his mother that he would now focus solely on Greek and Roman literature.

Mrs. Pendennis, ever indulgent, was delighted. “That’s wonderful!” she exclaimed. “But darling, don’t ruin your health. I’ve heard dreadful stories about students who overworked themselves and ended up with brain fever—some even died before finishing their degree.” Pen, though perfectly healthy (or so he claimed), kindly promised never to stay up late reading. And he kept this promise—with more determination than he showed in any other area of his life. That’s the thing about promises: they’re easiest to keep when they involve something you don’t actually want to do.

But soon Pen found the classical lectures duller than a Sunday sermon. His classmates were slow, and the tutor, Mr. Buck, was barely smarter than a fifth-form boy at Grey Friars. He could recite grammar rules about Aeschylus, but couldn’t appreciate poetry any better than Mrs. Binge, the bed-maker. Pen grew weary of watching these amateurs stumble through lines he could read in seconds. Eventually, he concluded that private reading was the only real education. So he told his mother he’d be reading alone far more often—and attending lectures far less. She, bless her, knew nothing about Homer or algebra, but trusted her son completely. “You’ll get the place you deserve,” she said, confident in his destiny.

Pen didn’t return home until after Christmas—slightly earlier than expected, which disappointed both his mother and Laura, who had been counting down the days to build a snow fortress, just like the one he’d built three winters ago. But he was invited to Logwood, Lady Agnes Foker’s estate, where there were private theatricals and a dazzling Christmas party full of fashionable people—people the Major insisted Pen must meet. He stayed home for the last three weeks of vacation, and Laura noticed immediately how many new clothes he’d brought: smart shooting jackets with flashy buttons, velvet waistcoats, embroidered cravats, and linen so fine it seemed to glow in the candlelight.

And then there was the dressing case—silver-mounted, gleaming like a treasure chest. Inside: rings, jewelry, and a French watch with a gold chain. Gone was the old chronometer that had hung from John Pendennis’s fob—the one with the jingling seals, once used by the late doctor to take patients’ pulses. Pen had coveted that watch just months earlier, thinking it the grandest timepiece in existence. Helen had given it to him with a touching speech about his father’s virtues and the importance of time. But now? Pen called it “outdated” and compared it to a warming-pan. Laura thought this disrespectful. He tucked it away in a drawer with old gloves, discarded cravats, and the school watch mentioned earlier.

Rebecca, his old horse, was also deemed insufficient. Pen traded her for a stronger, pricier steed. His father paid for it. Laura cried when Rebecca was led away.

Then came the cigars—boxes of them, labeled *Colorados*, *Afrancesados*, *Telescopios*, and *Fudson Oxford Street*. Pen smoked them everywhere: in the stables, near the greenhouses (where they helped fertilize Helen’s plants), and even in his study. At first, his mother disapproved. But Pen explained he was working on a prize poem and needed his cigar. He quoted Byron: “I smoke because I’m wise.” His mother, seeing him deep in thought, eventually relented. One day, she walked in to find him on the sofa, poring over a novel. Instead of interrupting, she quietly fetched his cigar case and lit it for him. Pen laughed, kissed her hand, and said, “Dear old mother, if I told you to burn the house down, I think you’d do it.” And honestly? He might have been right. She’d probably have done it.

Besides English “light literature,” Pen devoured French novels—books that left Helen wide-eyed when she glanced through them. But Pen defended himself: “It’s not my fault the books exist. I must read the best writers to improve my French.” And so, reluctantly, Mrs. Pendennis allowed it. Miss Laura, however, was strictly forbidden—both by her mother and by Arthur himself, who insisted that while he needed to study everything to become a well-rounded man, a young lady should not be exposed to such material.

During the long vacation, Pen drained the bin of claret his father had stockpiled—“Not a headache in a hogshead,” he’d once declared. When it ran out, he ordered more from Messrs. Binney and Latham of Mark Lane, London, recommended by the rector, Doctor Portman. “You’ll need to entertain your friends,” the rector had advised. “Use a respectable London firm—Oxbridge tradesmen charge exorbitant prices for poor-quality wine.” Pen followed the advice and wrote to Binney and Latham, requesting wine for both Fairoaks and his university account. When the bill arrived, his mother gasped. Pen laughed. “It’s moderate,” he said. “Everyone drinks claret and champagne now.” She paid it, knowing the household expenses were rising—but only briefly. Pen was home for a few weeks. While he was here, shouldn’t they make him happy?

Arthur’s allowance was generous—more than many sons of wealthier families received. Years earlier, John Pendennis had begun saving money for his son’s education, calling it the “Arthur Education Fund.” After his death, his widow added to it. By the time Arthur entered college, it was quite substantial. The Major believed in liberal allowances: “Let him enter society as a gentleman. If he fails later, it’ll be his own fault.” Stinting a boy, he said, was the worst policy. Arthur would face the world soon enough. For now, give him good habits, friends, and a strong start.

So Pen, the only son of a country gentleman, with a good allowance and a gentlemanly bearing, appeared far more important than he actually was. To the university authorities, tradesmen, and undergraduates alike, he was a true young buck—a member of the aristocracy. His manner was bold, cheerful, and perhaps a bit impertinent—just as a spirited youth should be. He was generous with money, which seemed limitless. He loved music, had a good voice, and enjoyed singing. Boat-racing hadn’t yet reached its current frenzy; riding and tandem-driving were the trends. Pen rode well to hounds, wore pink as befitted a dandy, and though not overly extravagant, still managed to rack up impressive bills at Nile’s livery stable and elsewhere.

In fact, Pen had almost every taste in life. He loved books—Doctor Portman had taught him to appreciate rare editions, and he adored beautiful bindings. His shelves became showcases of tall copies, gilded edges, marbled covers, and blind-tooling. He had a refined eye for art—prints by Strange, Rembrandt etchings, Wilkie originals—none of those cheap opera dancers or racing prints that had pleased Mr. Spicer. His rooms were furnished with impeccable taste, earning him respect among his peers.

He also had a weakness for jewelry, rings, and fine clothing. Pen was, by all accounts, a dressy man. He and his friends would prepare for dinner parties with the same care as if they were going to woo a queen. Rumor had it he wore rings over his kid gloves—he denies it, but what absurdities won’t youth commit with perfect seriousness? That he took perfumed baths? True. He claimed it was necessary after encountering certain “low-class” men in hall.

By his second year, when Miss Fotheringay became a sensation in London and dozens of prints were published of her, Pen hung one in his room. He confided to his friends how deeply, wildly, passionately he had loved her. He showed them verses he’d written—his brow darkened, eyes rolled, chest heaved. The verses spread through campus, copied, mocked, admired. There’s nothing that elevates a young man more than being seen as romantically tormented. They said he nearly committed suicide, fought a duel with a baronet. Freshmen pointed him out. When he swaggered out of college at two o’clock, surrounded by cronies, he was a sight to behold. Dressed to the nines, he’d ogle visiting ladies and comment on their beauty with the gravitas of a seasoned critic. People said, “I walked with Pendennis,” as if they’d shared a meal with a duke.

He and the Proctor exchanged nods like rivals—no one knew who held more power.

In short, Arthur Pendennis had become one of the leading figures at Oxbridge. Youth has a peculiar way of idolizing leaders. They follow, admire, imitate. Every generous boy has admired someone else—Pendennis had his followers, his loyal band, his rivals. When word spread at haberdashers that Mr. Pendennis had ordered a crimson satin cravat, within days, dozens appeared in Main Street. Simon the Jeweler sold two gross of “Pendennis pins” based on a design he’d chosen.

Now, if you’re the type who enjoys arithmetic, calculate how much a young man with such tastes would spend in two or three years. It adds up fast. Pen didn’t have a calculating mind, but none of his habits were outrageously wasteful. Still, look at the bills: Paddington’s tailor, Guttlebury’s cook, Dillon Tandy’s print purchases, Wormall’s book orders—each far exceeded Pen’s own modest expenses. Yet Pendennis stood apart. While Lord Paddington cared nothing for art, Guttlebury ignored fashion, Snaffle read only racing guides, and others focused narrowly—Pen embraced it all. He excelled in each, not as a specialist, but as a polymath of pleasure.

And so, with style, flair, and a heart full of romantic nonsense, Pendennis of Boniface ruled the roost—at least until the next season, when the next dashing young man would take his place.

CHAPTER XX. Rake’s Progress (Rewritten for Humor)

Sometime before Mr. Foker packed his bags and fled Oxbridge like a man escaping from a bad dinner party, a certain gentleman arrived at Boniface College—though not without a bit of scandal attached to his past.
