This was Horace Bloundell, a name that sounded suspiciously like a family crest someone had scribbled on a napkin after too many claret cups. He hailed from the ancient Suffolk line of Bloundell-Bloundell, of Bloundell-Bloundell Hall, in Bloundell-Bloundellshire—a place so rich in hyphens it could have been written by a grammarian with a grudge against simplicity. No one quite knew why Boniface took him in, especially since St. George’s and several other colleges had politely declined him, but perhaps it was because Dr. Donne, the Master, was also from Suffolk and may have shared a distant cousin or two with the Bloundells—or maybe he just liked the sound of “Bloundell-Bloundell” when spoken with a flourish.

Mr. Bloundell had once been a dragoon—crimson pantaloons, sable shakos, the whole military kit—and had served bravely until his third brother, who was supposed to inherit the family living, fell ill and died. At which point, Mr. Bloundell decided to swap his sword for a surplice, his sabre for a sermon, and become an English divine. A noble ambition, you might say—except for the fact that he’d already left Camford under a cloud of suspicion involving tutors, a stolen quill pen, and possibly a pigeon. But undeterred, he came to Oxbridge with the same fierce determination as a man trying to win a game of poker with only one card.

During Pen’s second year, Major Pendennis made a brief visit to see his nephew and met the young men who hung around his rooms. There was Lord Plinlimmon, gentle as a lamb and polite as a teacup; Magnus Charters, gallant and open-hearted (though prone to quoting Rousseau while wearing a cravat); Harland, sly and witty, whose Greek quotations were more confusing than enlightening; Ringwood, known as Rupert in the Union Debating Club because of his daring opinions and even bolder blunders; Broadbent, nicknamed Barebones Broadbent for his republican views and his habit of wearing boots that looked like they’d seen a revolution; and finally, Mr. Bloundell-Bloundell, who had somehow managed to insert himself into the inner circle of university society within days.

Major Pendennis, though he didn’t understand half of what Harland said (and frankly, didn’t care), enjoyed the company—except for one particular young man who seemed to believe he was a character in a novel rather than a student in a college. This man, of course, was Mr. Bloundell, who walked about with the air of a man who had been to Paris, seen everything, and was now merely waiting for the world to bow down.

At breakfast the next morning, the Major gave his verdict on the lot. He praised Pen’s dinner (“Merveille! Though a tad too fine, my boy”), complimented Lord Plinlimmon’s resemblance to his mother (Lady Aquila Brownbill, whom he’d once danced with at a ball), and said Lord Magnus would grow out of his republicanism—“though I wouldn’t want to sit next to him during a coronation.” He admired Broadbent’s eloquence and Foker’s charm, but then turned sharply to Pen.

“And your friend Bloundell?” he asked, peeling an egg with the precision of a surgeon. “He struck me as the most ineligible young man I’ve ever met.”

“Bless my soul, sir!” Pen laughed. “Bloundell-Bloundell? Why, he’s the most popular man in the university! We elected him to the Barmecides the very first week—he’s of an excellent family, descended from Richard’s Blondel, bear a harp in chief—and motto O Mong Roy!”

“A man may have a coat-of-arms,” said the Major, “but still be a tiger. And this fellow is a tiger, mark my words. Low. I’ll lay odds he left his regiment in disgrace—though I admit, it was a good one. My friend Lord Martingale never sat in a saddle without looking like a king. But this Bloundell? He wears too many rings. Too much jewellery. That’s the sign of a scamp. He haunts low gambling-houses, billiard-hells, third-rate clubs—I know it. I can tell a man’s character by his style. And I’ve never been wrong yet. Did you notice how he carried himself? Like a man who’s used to winning, but not used to paying.”

Pen, however, was not convinced. “But sir, he’s charming! Everyone likes him. Even the proctors seem to nod at him.”

“Then let them nod,” said the Major. “But don’t you follow him. He’s a wolf in velvet. Mark my words.”

Poor Pen. The worthy uncle had no idea how often those dinners actually happened. In truth, Pen was hosting them every other night, like a modern-day Bacchus with a budget. There’s nothing boys love more than pretending to know about wine and cuisine—it gives them an air of sophistication, like they’re part of some secret society of epicures. They wink at claret as if it were a lover, discuss salmi with solemnity, and imagine themselves dining with kings. But alas, as they grow older, they realize they know nothing—except that a leg of mutton and a quiet heart are far better than a table full of truffles and regret.

And Pen, in his role as the Admirable Crichton of Oxford, was determined to be the greatest host in all the land. His staircase became a veritable circus: waiters, dish-covers, skips with iced champagne, and a constant stream of piteous-faced servants begging for tips or complaining about broken silverware. It was chaos—and Pen loved every minute of it.

The Major’s advice? Forgotten. Pen continued to hang around Bloundell, who, despite his dubious reputation, had one thing going for him: he *knew* life. Or at least, he claimed to. A man who speaks of Paris, garrison conquests, and the Palais Royal with confidence is always welcome among students who’ve never been farther than the library. Pen, in particular, adored stories of adventure—especially if they involved money, women, and dice.

Bloundell, of course, was an expert at all three.

One night, after a supper-party where vingt-et-un had been played (a genteel alternative to singing songs, which always attracted proctors like flies to honey), Bloundell did something remarkable. He picked up a green wine-glass meant for iced punch, slipped in a pair of dice, and began tossing them with the grace of a man who’d spent years perfecting the art. “Sevens the main!” he declared, and with a flourish, sent the ivory cubes flying across the table. Then he sang the chorus from *Robert the Devil*, and soon everyone was playing hazard, the glass passing from hand to hand until Pen accidentally shattered it—after rolling six mains, naturally.

From that moment, Pen was hooked. Dice became his new passion. They played mornings, afternoons, evenings—any time the bones could rattle. Bloundell even invented a felt-lined box so the noise wouldn’t betray them to the tutors. Once, they nearly got caught by Mr. Buck, who heard “Two to one on the caster” through Pen’s window. But when he entered, he found three Homers spread across the table and Pen asking gravely, “What is the current state of the River Scamander, sir? Is it navigable?”

Mr. Buck, baffled, replied, “I believe it flows, Mr. Pendennis.”

Pen nodded sagely. “Ah, yes. Navigable, then.”

And thus, the dice game continued.

Pen didn’t win much money—though he learned the odds of hazard better than any book could teach. But Bloundell had other talents. He told stories—fabulous, improbable tales of nights in Paris, of winning twelve thousand francs under his pillow, of finding a hundred and forty-nine Napoleons in his boot. Pen listened wide-eyed, believing every word. He respected the man who could win such sums—what a coup, indeed!

When Pen confessed his love for Miss Fotheringay, Bloundell laughed. “Marry her?” he sneered. “You might as well marry the actress at the Theatre Royal. She hasn’t a shred of character. Twenty men are openly courting her. I know them all—here’s the list.”

And so he named them, along with the amounts each had spent. Pen was stunned. His pure flame, his noble passion—was it all a lie? He didn’t question Bloundell. After all, Bloundell never spoke well of women. So why should he doubt him?

Later, during Easter vacation, Pen went to London with Bloundell. They stayed at a hotel in Covent Garden, where Bloundell had a “tick”—meaning he owed the landlord a fortune but was still allowed to stay. They dined at military clubs, drank pale ale, and beat the town like two young lions loose in a zoo. One night, Pen saw Miss Fotheringay perform—at the Museum Theatre, no less. He was seated in the boxes, separated from Major Pendennis by only a plank of wood. The Major was in Lord Steyne’s box, watching the show with a look of mild disapproval.

Fotheringay was in full glory. She had drawn good houses, starred in the provinces, and returned to London with slightly dimmed luster—but still enough to fill the theatre. Pen watched her. He remembered the day they parted in Chatteris. He remembered how he used to watch for her entrance, how her voice used to thrill him.

But now? The light was gone from her eyes. Her voice was loud, but mechanical. The brogue beneath was coarse. He realized, with horror, that he’d once mistaken imitation for genius. He felt ashamed. Lonely. As if he’d lived a dream and woken up to find it was all a hoax.

After the play, they had a wild supper. Pen woke the next morning with a headache and an empty purse. He returned to Oxbridge, broke, humiliated, and utterly disillusioned.

As for his finances? Well, Pen never kept track. He spent money like water, and trusted everyone. Even Finch, the money-lender Bloundell introduced him to, treated him kindly—never charging more than a hundred per cent interest. The old college cook, who adored him, sent dinners up to the very end and never pressed for payment. Pen was honest, generous, and utterly naive. He’d share a guinea with a poor friend, pay off debts with reckless cheerfulness, and leave parties to sit with sick comrades.

In short, he was the kind of man who could charm a thief into giving him back his wallet.

So while he may have been ruined by Bloundell’s influence, he remained beloved by most. For all his follies, there was something about Arthur Pendennis that made people forgive him—even when he deserved it least. And perhaps, in the end, that was his greatest fault—and his greatest strength.

Ah, the noble family of Pendennis—famed in heraldry for their crest: a mother pelican, noble and generous, bleeding from her own bosom to feed her brood of greedy little chicks. A touching image, isn’t it? And yet, if you’ve ever known a woman—especially a mother—you’ll recognize the truth in it. Women, bless them, seem to find pleasure in self-sacrifice. Not that we men understand it. To us, being stabbed by one’s own child would be sheer agony. But women? They practically volunteer for it. They’d rather bleed than not love. They pour out their hearts, their fortunes, their very souls, for the sake of someone they adore—be it a husband who spends more than he earns, a son who flirts with ruin, or a brother who’s clearly destined for a life of mild scandal.

And what do we men do in return? We enjoy it. Oh yes. We revel in their forgiveness. We know full well that if we’re caught doing something stupid—say, gambling away the rent money or getting into a duel over a hat—we can always count on the soft creatures to forgive us. They don’t mind living in threadbare gowns; but when the prodigal returns, they throw a feast. The fatted calf is slaughtered. Mercy and Forgiveness come out grinning like two benevolent clowns at a village fair. If only justice were our only companion, we’d all be doomed. Heaven help us.

Now, during Arthur Pendennis’s time at Oxbridge, his uncle—the Major—had grown quite fond of him. The old bachelor, who never entertained friends (and whose stinginess was a standing joke among the Club wags, who envied his many engagements and failed to notice his poverty), had taken an unusual shine to young Pen. He liked Arthur’s high spirits, his good looks, and that gentlemanly air he carried himself with. It delighted the Major to see his nephew walking arm-in-arm with the sons of noblemen, and so he began hosting little dinners at his lodgings—snug affairs, with claret flowing freely, and stories so sharp they could cut glass. Some of these tales, if repeated, would lose their charm—because the Major told them with such precision and flair. Others? Well, let’s just say no one should repeat them at all.

He flattered the parents of these young lords through their sons, and in doing so, flattered himself. He made several trips to Oxbridge, where the students amused themselves by entertaining the old fellow—hosting parties, breakfasts, and even small fetes, half in mockery, half in genuine respect. The Major, eager to please, regaled them with stories, acted like a man half his age, and once even attended a grand debate at the Union. When Pen spoke, the Major roared, cheered, and banged his cane in time with the crowd. He was thunderstruck by the boy’s eloquence. “I’ve got a young Pitt!” he declared. He doted on Pen like a father. He wrote letters full of playful advice and gossip from London. He bragged about Arthur at every Club he visited, introducing him proudly as “my boy,” and declaring that the new generation—Lord Plinlimmon, Lord Magnus Charters, and others—would outshine their fathers.

He even asked permission to bring Arthur to Gaunt House, where he watched him dance with the daughters of the nobility, and later went to great lengths to secure invitations for him—like some proud mamma with a daughter to marry, except this was an old half-pay officer in a wig.

And then came the downfall.

Pen’s extravagance, his idle ways, his reckless spending—these things crushed the dreams of both his uncle and his mother. And now, as he stood alone in his room, staring at the ceiling, he felt the weight of it all. He had slept while the tortoise won the race. He had ruined a career that might have been brilliant. He had dipped into his mother’s purse like a thief, and emptied it with careless abandon. O, what a cowardly hand it must have been that struck down such a tender creature!

But worse than the guilt he felt for betraying others was the shame he felt for himself. A vain young man like Arthur Pendennis—what could be more painful than the wound of his own pride? There’s no greater torment than that. No groan more pitiful than the cry of wounded self-love.

Like Joel Miller’s friend, the Senior Wrangler, who bowed to the audience because he entered the theatre at the same time as the king—though, unlike that gentleman, Pen didn’t feel any grace in his moment of glory—he was certain that all England would notice his name was missing from the examination list. His tutor, his creditors, the skip and bed-maker who waited on him, the undergraduates he’d patronized or mocked—how could he face any of them?

He fled. Into his rooms he shut himself, and there penned a letter to his tutor—full of thanks, regret, despair, and a request to remove his name from the college books. He hinted darkly that death might soon end his suffering. Then, without knowing where he was going, he wandered through the back lanes of the university, past the colleges, until he reached the banks of the Camisis River, once alive with boat races and cheering crowds, now silent and deserted.

As he climbed a hill, a drizzling January rain soaking his face and his ragged gown fluttering behind him, a postchaise rattled up the road. On the box sat a servant, and inside—half out the window—a young gentleman smoking a cigar and shouting orders to the postboy. It was Mr. Spavin, that familiar figure from Baymouth, who had just passed his degree and was returning home in triumph in his yellow carriage.

Spavin saw Pen, pale and wild-eyed, struggling up the hill. “Wo!” he bellowed to the postboy. The horses stopped. The chaise pulled up fifty yards ahead.

“Hey! Pen!” Spavin shouted, leaning out the window. “Where are you off to?”

Pen stopped, hesitated, nodded fiercely, and pointed forward—pretending he wanted the coach to go on. He said nothing. But his face must have looked dire enough, because Spavin, alarmed, jumped out, ran to him, and grasped his hand.

“I say—hullo, old boy! What’s the row?” he asked.

“I’m going where I deserve to go,” Pen muttered, almost cursing.

“This ain’t the way,” Spavin said, smiling. “This is the Fenbury road. Don’t take on because you’re plucked. It’s nothing. I’ve been plucked three times. After the first, I didn’t care. Glad it’s over, though. You’ll do better next time.”

Pen stared at him—this man who’d failed repeatedly, who’d been rusticated, who’d only learned to read and write properly after years of effort—and yet had managed to get a degree.

“This man has passed,” thought Pen, “and I have failed.” It nearly broke him.

“Good-bye, Spavin,” he said. “I’m glad you made it. Don’t let me keep you—I’m in a hurry. Going to town tonight.”

“Gammon,” said Spavin. “This ain’t the way to town. This is the Fenbury road. I tell you.”

“I was just turning back,” Pen said.

“All the coaches are full,” Spavin said. “You wouldn’t get a seat for ten pounds. Get in my yellow. I’ll drop you at Mudford. I’ve got hats and coats—jump in, old boy—go it, leathers!”

And so, against his will, Pen found himself in Spavin’s postchaise, riding toward Mudford, fifteen miles from Oxbridge, where he boarded the Fenbury mail to London.

The next day, Boniface College was in an uproar. Rumors flew that Pendennis, broken-hearted by failing his exams, had thrown himself into the mill-stream near Fenbury Road. A battered cap with his name barely legible, and a seal bearing the crest of an eagle gazing at a dead sun, had been found nearby. For twenty-four hours, everyone believed he was dead—until letters arrived from him, stamped with the London postmark.

The mail reached London at five o’clock. Pen rushed to his usual inn in Covent Garden. The porter admitted him without a word. In bed, he couldn’t sleep. He tossed until dawn, then rose and walked to his uncle’s lodgings in Bury Street.

The maid, scrubbing the steps, glanced up suspiciously at his unshaven face and yesterday’s linen. He thought she knew.

“Good ’evens, Mr. Harthur,” said Morgan, the valet, who was arranging clothes and boots at the door. “What as ’appened, sir?”

“I want to see my uncle,” Pen said, voice cracking. He collapsed onto a chair.

Morgan backed away, terrified, and disappeared into the bedroom.

The Major poked his head out, wig freshly on. “What? Examination over? Senior Wrangler, double First Class, hay?” he called cheerfully. “I’ll be right out.”

Then he saw Pen’s face. His hand dropped. “Good God, Pen—what’s the matter?”

“You’ll see it in the papers at breakfast,” Pen said.

“See what?”

“My name isn’t there, sir.”

“Hang it, why should it be?” the Major replied, puzzled.

“I’ve lost everything, sir,” Pen groaned. “My honour’s gone. I’m ruined. I can’t go back.”

“Lost your honour?” the Major shrieked. “Heaven alive! You don’t mean you showed the white feather?”

Pen laughed bitterly. “No, sir. Not that. I’m not afraid of being shot. I wish someone would. I didn’t get my degree. I’m plucked, sir.”

The Major had heard of “plucking,” but assumed it was some kind of physical punishment meted out to rebellious students.

“I wonder you can look me in the face after such a disgrace,” he said. “I wonder you submitted to it as a gentleman.”

“I couldn’t help it,” Pen said. “I did the classics well enough. It was the infernal mathematics. I’ve always neglected them.”

“Was it—was it done in public?” the Major asked anxiously.

“What?”

“The plucking?”

Pen realized his uncle had no idea what “plucking” meant. A faint smile touched his lips. He explained: he’d taken the exam and failed.

“Oh,” said the Major. “Well, though I expected better, it’s not the end of the world. No dishonour, as far as I see. Try again.”

“Me again at Oxbridge,” Pen thought, “after that humiliation?” He felt he could never return—unless he set fire to the place.

But it was when he confessed his debts that the Major truly exploded. Pen had compiled a full list: tailor bills, horse hire, haberdashers, jewelers, books, wine merchants, hotel charges, sundries—everything. Altogether, about seven hundred pounds. And he’d spent more than twice that in ready cash.

“You needn’t press a man who’s down,” Pen said, gloomily. “I know how wicked and idle I’ve been. My mother won’t like seeing me disgraced. But I won’t ask her for another penny.”

“As you like,” the Major said. “You’re of age. My hands are washed. But you can’t live without money. And I see no way for you to earn it—though you’ve shown a fine talent for spending it. I fear you’ll go on as you’ve begun, and ruin your mother before you’re five years older.”

He pulled on his hat, trembling slightly, and walked out, leaving Pen alone.

That evening, the Major read the examination lists in the paper. He consulted various old fogies at the Club—Wenham, a Dean, a few Civilians—showing them the debt list and asking what to do. They assured him such debts were common. Young men owed twice, five times as much. The only solution? Pay.

So the Major wrote to Doctor Portman, asking him to break the news to Helen. Because, as the Major believed, it was more proper to deliver bad news through a messenger—possibly clumsy, possibly heartless—than to send a note.

He then went to dinner, the saddest man in London.

Pen, meanwhile, wrote his letter to his mother—full of remorse, tenderness, tears. He read a book near the Temple, saw prints at Ackermann’s, went to the play, and forgot the name of the piece. He avoided the Oxbridge boys at his hotel, pacing the streets like a ghost.

On the second day, his tutor wrote, urging him not to leave the university. “It was carelessness,” he said. “A month of study, and you can recover.”

On the third day, a letter came from home. Pen read it in his room. Then he fell to his knees, head buried in the bedclothes, and prayed—truly prayed. Later, after eating a massive breakfast, he marched to the Bull and Mouth, Piccadilly, and boarded the Chatteris coach for that evening.

And so, Arthur Pendennis left Oxbridge—not with glory, but with a heavy heart, a heavier debt, and a future still uncertain. But the journey had only just begun.

**CHAPTER XXII. The Prodigal’s Return (Or, How a Son Came Home and Was Forgiven—And Also, How His Sister Nearly Bankrupted the Family)**

When Major Pendennis wrote that letter—oh, what a letter!—it was like dropping a bombshell into the quiet village of Clavering. Doctor Portman, ever the moral compass of the district, didn’t hesitate. He packed his bags with the urgency of a man who’d just been told he’d forgotten to pay his taxes *and* had a toothache. Off he went to Fairoaks, not because he liked bad news, but because he believed in delivering it with the dignity of a man who’d once held a position in the Church and had seen enough sin to last a lifetime.

He arrived at the Pendennis home with all the solemnity of a funeral procession, though no one had died—yet. He read the letter aloud, pausing dramatically between sentences as if each word were a verdict from God Himself. “The young scapegrace,” he declared, “is going deeper and deeper into perdition!” He added several more such remarks, which sounded very impressive, especially when delivered in a voice that suggested he might have been born in a cathedral.

Afterward, he left the ladies to spread the news through Clavering society—where gossip travels faster than a horse with a broken leg—and strode off to Fairoaks to break the news to the widow herself. But she already knew. She’d read Pen’s letter, and oddly enough, it had brought her relief. For months, a shadow had hung over her—a dark, brooding suspicion that her son was spiraling toward ruin. Now, she knew the worst. And what did she find? Her darling boy had returned, repentant, tender-hearted, and full of regret. What more could a mother ask for?

Doctor Portman, bless his soul, tried to lecture her on virtue, duty, and the importance of degrees. But Helen Pendennis wasn’t having it. “What good is a degree?” she asked, quite reasonably. “It won’t feed him, nor clothe him, nor keep him from being laughed at by those who don’t know him.” She went on to question why anyone would send a boy to Oxford when there was so much temptation, so little benefit, and so many places where he could be properly supervised—like, say, *home*. As for debts? “His father’s money was his!” she insisted. “And if he spent it, well—wasn’t that what money was for?”

So while Doctor Portman stood there, flustered and indignant, like a man who’d just discovered his favorite hat was made of paper, the widow simply smiled and said, “I’m glad he’s back.”

Meanwhile, Miss Laura Bell—now sixteen years old and about as tall as a maypole with roses growing around its top—had also been keeping an eye on her brother. Once upon a time, they’d shared affectionate embraces, kisses, and whispered secrets. But now, after college, Arthur had returned to find that the little girl he’d left behind had become a young lady—tall, graceful, and suddenly too dignified for cheek-kisses. Instead, she greeted him with a curtsey, a blush, and a hand offered like a peace offering. It was awkward. It was sweet. It was the kind of moment that makes you wish you’d stayed away.

Laura, though, had grown up fast. She’d developed opinions—especially about her brother’s behavior. His letters had become shorter, colder, more like postcards from a distant land where nobody cared about mothers. “It’s better he loses a prize,” she’d say, “than forgets his mother.” She didn’t understand why he couldn’t come home instead of spending vacations at the grand houses of his rich friends. “Nobody there loves him half as much as you do,” she’d tell her mother.

Helen would sigh. “But he must make his way in the world,” she’d murmur, trying to sound convincing even to herself.

Laura wouldn’t hear it. She thought Arthur had become too free with his talk—too sarcastic, too dismissive of women in general. He smoked pipes indoors. He refused to go to church. He yawned during novels while wearing a dressing-gown that looked like it had seen better days. Worst of all, he’d fallen for an actress—Miss Fotheringay, a woman whose name alone made Laura want to throw a teacup.

“A Pendennis!” she gasped. “Falling for *that* woman? Galloping off every day to kneel at her feet and drink with her horrid father? And then bringing them both into our house? I’d have run away, mamma—I’d have walked barefoot through snow!”

“Then you’d have left me,” Helen replied gently.

And so, with tears in her eyes, Laura withdrew her statement, and the two women embraced like sisters who’d just survived a hurricane.

Now, here’s the thing: Laura didn’t hate Arthur. Not really. She was angry—at his choices, at his pride, at his reckless ways. But when she heard he was miserable, ill, and ashamed, her anger vanished like smoke in the wind. In an instant, she became the most devoted sister imaginable. She took Pen’s side against Doctor Portman, who had the audacity to speak of “transgressions” and “moral failings.”

“Debts?” Laura scoffed. “They’re nothing! He was thrown into expensive society by his uncle—what was he supposed to do, eat bread and water while others dined on caviar? And as for the degree—poor boy, he was sick! Can you think of math when your mind is full of creditors?”

She glared at the doctor, seized her mother’s hand, and kissed her passionately in front of him. “How dare you speak against him?” she seemed to say with her eyes. “He’s my brother. My darling Pen.”

Doctor Portman left, slightly stunned, wondering if he’d just been outwitted by a pair of women who clearly didn’t believe in logic.

Once he was gone, Laura sprang into action. Fires were lit in Arthur’s room. His bedding was aired. And while Helen penned a tender letter to her son, Laura joined her, led her into the room, and sat beside her on the bed like two conspirators plotting a revolution.

Then, in a fit of inspiration, Laura added a postscript:
> “My dearest Pen—come home instantly! Two dashes under ‘Pen’ for emphasis. Be happy with your mother and your affectionate sister Laura.”

That night, as the two women lay awake—reading their Bibles and checking on Pen’s room—Laura suddenly sat up.
“Mamma,” she whispered, “are you awake?”

“Yes,” Helen murmured, though she hadn’t slept a wink. She’d been staring at the night-lamp, thinking of Pen.

Then Laura revealed her brilliant plan. “You know, mamma,” she said, “I’ve lived with you for ten years. You’ve never taken any of my money. That’s offended me. I’m proud. I don’t like being beholden.”

“And?” Helen asked, intrigued.

“Well,” Laura continued, “if I’d gone to school, it would’ve cost fifty pounds a year. Ten years times fifty—that’s five hundred pounds! I know you put it in the bank at Chatteris. So tomorrow—we’ll go to Chatteris. We’ll see Mr. Rowdy, the bald-headed banker, and ask for it. And I bet he’ll give us two more. We’ll save it, send it to Pen, and he can pay his debts without hurting anyone. Then we’ll live happily ever after.”

Helen’s response was a mix of laughter, tears, and frantic hugs. The plan was absurd—but somehow, perfectly logical.

And so, when Pen returned, the house was warm, welcoming, and full of love. He stepped down from the coach, shivering in the January cold, refusing an inside seat because he wanted to suffer for his sins. He met Old John, who had traded his livery for a fustian jacket and now worked as gardener and stable-man. “I’m in the lodge now,” he said proudly, grinning.

But as soon as Pen turned the corner, Helen appeared—beaming, forgiving, ready to embrace him as if he’d only been away for a week.

She’d already written him a letter detailing Laura’s noble offer, complete with praise and blessings for both children.

Pen blushed when he saw Laura, who waited in the hall. This time, she broke their long-standing rule and kissed him—just once. And then, wisely, stopped. There had been *too much* kissing already in this chapter.

The prodigal was home. The fatted calf was slaughtered (metaphorically). And he was made as happy as two simple women could possibly make him.

No one mentioned the Oxbridge disaster. No one asked what he’d do next. But Pen, restless and remorseful, couldn’t stop thinking about it.

A few days later, he rode to Chatteris, then came back on the top of the coach. He sold his horse and handed over the cheque. It was a gesture of virtue—or perhaps just guilt. Laura called it “strict justice.” Pen avoided talking about the loan, though he thanked her once or twice, stammering like a man who’d just been caught stealing.

He gave up wine, switched to whisky-and-water (with great moderation), quit cigars (though he’d always preferred pipes anyway), and fell asleep frequently after dinner. He watched coaches like a hawk, read newspapers obsessively, played cribbage with Captain Glanders, and avoided Doctor Portman like the plague.

But he went to church regularly. He read prayers for his mother. He kept the house humble—two maids, no silver dish-covers, John in his livery only on Sundays. It was a life of austerity, and it made him feel worse.

For a while, he swore he’d never return to Oxford. But then Laura, blushing furiously, suggested he go back—not for glory, but for redemption. “As punishment,” she said, “you should get your degree.”

And so, with a heavy heart, Pen returned.

At Oxford, he was a ghost. A plucked bird. No longer part of any set, no longer known by name. He went to chapel every morning, shut himself in his rooms at night, and studied like a man trying to win back something he’d lost.

There were no duns at his door—his debts were paid. No cards left. His classmates had graduated and moved on. He took a second examination—and passed easily.

When he returned in his bachelor’s gown, he felt a little lighter. He visited his uncle in London, but the old man barely acknowledged him. One visit, he got a cold nod. The second? Morgan the valet said, “Master’s not home.”

Pen returned to Fairoaks, to books, to silence, to loneliness.

He wrote tragedies. Poems about despair. He considered enlisting—Spain, the Legion, anything to escape. He cursed idleness, though it was idleness that had brought him home.

Helen worried. “He’s breaking his heart,” she said. “We must get him out of here.”

And so, when they could afford it, he would go abroad. Or to London. Anywhere but this quiet house with two women who loved him too much—and whose sadness began to deepen with each passing day.

For the first time, Laura noticed how pale her mother had become. How tired. How the light seemed to fade from her eyes.

And Pen? He was still there—repentant, penitent, and quietly dying of boredom.

CHAPTER XXIII. New Faces (Rewritten for Humor)

The residents of Fairoaks were minding their own business—mostly sleeping, eating, and occasionally wondering if the sky was really that blue or just a trick of the light—when across the River Brawl, atop a hill so grand it looked like it had been built by giants with bad posture, something astonishing happened: Clavering Park woke up.

Not metaphorically. Literally. Like a giant cat stretching after a long nap, the great house yawned, dusted off its cobwebs, and declared, “I’m back, and I’ve brought a suitcase full of drama.”

This occurred just as Arthur Pendennis was still reeling from his recent mishap—some minor disaster involving a horse, a fence, and possibly a lost glove—which left him so emotionally shattered he barely noticed the world around him. But others did. And they weren’t exactly thrilled.

An announcement appeared in the local papers—one of those provincial rags that seem to exist solely to report on aristocratic weddings, sheep thefts, and the sudden return of long-lost baronets—and the whole county went into a tizzy. From Clavering Market to Cackleby Fair, from Gooseberry Green to Tinkleton Church gate, everyone was whispering: *Clavering Park is being lived in again.*

Five years earlier, the same paper had reported the marriage of Francis Clavering, Esq., only son of Sir Francis Clavering, Bart., to Jemima Augusta Snell, widow of J. Amory, Esq., and daughter of Samuel Snell, Esq., of Calcutta. At the time, folks thought it was a sad but sensible move: ruined nobleman marries rich widow from India. A classic case of “money solves everything except existential dread.”

And indeed, it did. The newlyweds were spotted at various continental hotspots—Florence, Schloss Schinkenstein, Rome, Lucca, Nice, even gambling dens along the Rhine. They were seen dining, dancing, and generally behaving like people who’d forgotten what modesty meant. Their final stop? Paris, where they lived like kings, thanks to the death of Mr. Snell, whose estate apparently included enough gold to fund a small country.

But before we get ahead of ourselves, let’s talk about Sir Francis Clavering Sr.—a man whose life story reads like a cautionary tale written by a disgruntled uncle. Born to an outlaw in a damp chateau near Bruges, young Francis tried to join a dragoon regiment, failed spectacularly, got himself bankrupt at the gambling table, spent two years in the army, sold out, ended up in the Fleet Prison, then fled to Ostend to live with his gout-ridden father. For years, he wandered Europe like a particularly well-dressed ghost, haunting billiard rooms, riding other people’s horses in steeplechases, and punting at gambling houses with the confidence of someone who doesn’t believe in consequences.

Then came the lucky coup: he met Jemima Augusta Snell at a boarding house in Lausanne. She was recently returned from Calcutta, widowed, wealthy, and reportedly not above marrying a man just because he wore a good hat. They married. Her father died shortly after, leaving her a fortune so large it made bankers weep with joy. And since she now had a title—Lady Clavering—her allowance doubled. Because nothing says “respectability” like a peerage and a trust fund.

Now, Lady Clavering wasn’t exactly the darling of polite society. Back in Calcutta, her family hadn’t been invited to Government House. Her father was a disreputable lawyer and indigo smuggler. Her first husband, Amory, was the mate of the Indiaman that brought her to India. Old Sir Jasper Rogers, Chief Justice of Calcutta, once said he could tell a “queer story” about her first husband—but never did. That mystery haunted dinner parties for decades.

Still, when Lady Clavering moved into the Hotel Bouilli in Paris, suddenly everyone wanted to be her friend. The Faubourg St. Germain embraced her. Viscount Bagwig, our excellent ambassador, paid her attention. Princes attended her salons. Even the most rigid English ladies—Lady Elderbury, Lady Rockminster, the Countess of Southdown—broke their moral fasts to attend her soirées. Why? Because she had money. Ten thousand pounds a year (some said twenty). And with that kind of cash, you can buy forgiveness, respect, and a subscription list longer than the King’s.

She funded charities like they were going out of style. Jesuits? Five Napoleons. Armenian patriarchs? Done. Baptist missions to Quashyboo? No problem. Orthodox settlements in Feefawfoo, the largest cannibal island in the Pacific? She sent them a check and a note saying, “Please don’t eat the missionaries.” And one day, she gave five Napoleons to Madame de Cricri for the persecuted Jesuits… and the very next moment wrote a check to Reverend J. Ramshorn, who believed God had instructed him to convert the Pope. Yes, she supported both sides. That’s how generous she was.

And during this time, she must have settled her husband’s debts in England, because Sir Francis reappeared—unhindered by bailiffs or guilt—announced in the Morning Post, and drove up the long avenue of Clavering Park in a carriage pulled by four horses, like a man who’d just won the lottery and the war.

Three men were in the carriage: Mr. Tatham of Chatteris (the little lawyer), a portly gentleman with whiskers, fur collars, and a mustache so thick it could double as a curtain, and a pale, weak-looking man who seemed to need a nap mid-arrival.

They reached the moss-covered steps. The foreign attendant with earrings and a gold-laced cap rang the bell with the enthusiasm of a man announcing the end of the world. Inside, the house groaned under its own weight. Then, footsteps echoed through the hall, and Mrs. Blenkinsop, the housekeeper, emerged with Polly, her aide-de-camp, and Smart, the keeper.

Smart kicked his heel as if a dog were biting him. Mrs. Blenkinsop curtsied. Polly curtsied and bowed rapidly, like a nervous bird. Mrs. Blenkinsop, trembling, said, “Welcome to Clavering, Sir Francis. It does my poor eyes good to see one of the family once more.”

She meant the grand gentleman in fur and braiding—the one who looked like he’d just stepped out of a royal opera. But he burst out laughing. “You’ve saddled the wrong horse, old lady! I’m not Sir Francis. Behold your rightful lord!”

He pointed at the pale, languid man, who muttered, “Don’t be an ass, Ned.”

“Oh,” said the pale man, “yes, Mrs. Blenkinsop. I’m Sir Francis Clavering. Remember me? Forgot me, I suppose? How do you do?”

He took her hand gently, nodded kindly, and added, “Dismal old place, ain’t it, Ned? Never saw it but once, when my governor quarrelled with grandfather in ’twenty-three.”

“Dismal?” said Ned. “Beautiful! The Castle of Otranto! The Mysteries of Udolpho! By Jove! What a fireplace! You might roast an elephant in it. Splendid carved gallery! Inigo Jones, by Jove! I’d lay five to two it’s Inigo Jones.”

“The upper part by Inigo Jones,” said Mrs. Blenkinsop. “The lower was altered by Vanderputty, the Dutch architect, in George the First’s time.”

“O indeed,” said Sir Francis. “Gad, Ned, you know everything.”

“I know a few things, Frank,” Ned replied. “I know that’s not a Snyders over the mantelpiece—bet you three to one it’s a copy. We’ll restore it. A lick of varnish, and it’ll come out wonderfully.”

“And that fellow in red?” asked Ned.

“Sheriff of the county,” said Mrs. Blenkinsop. “Served in Parliament under Queen Anne. That on the right is Theodosia, wife of Harbottle, second baronet, painted by Lely as Venus. Her son Gregory, third baronet, as Cupid. Next panel: Sir Rupert, knighted by Charles the First, whose lands were confiscated by Oliver Cromwell.”

“Thank you,” said Sir Francis. “We’ll walk about ourselves. Frosch, give me a cigar. Have a cigar, Mr. Tatham?”

Mr. Tatham accepted one, spluttered violently, and coughed like a dying steam engine.

“No need to come with us, Mrs. Blenkinsop. Smart—feed the horses and wash their mouths. Shan’t stay long. Come along, Strong—I know the way. Was here in ’twenty-three.”

So Sir Francis and Captain Strong—his friend and self-appointed architect of revival—walked through the house like explorers in a museum of melancholy. Rooms stretched endlessly. Saloons, drawing rooms, oak room, library (where Pen once found books), grand dining room, library again—each one bigger than the last, each one emptier.

Captain Strong, however, was enchanted. “I see capabilities in it—capabilities, sir!” he cried. “Leave it to me, and I’ll make it the pride of the county at a small expense. What a theatre we can have in the library—curtains between the columns! What a galop room! Hold the whole shire! Hang the morning parlour with the tapestry from your Rue de Grenelle salon. Furnish the oak room with Moyen-age cabinets and armour. Armour looks splendid against black oak. There’s a Venice glass in the Quai Voltaire—perfect for that mantelpiece.”

“I recollect my old governor caning me in that little room,” said Sir Francis. “He always hated me.”

“Chintz is the dodge for my lady’s rooms,” said Strong. “The suite on the south landing—bedroom, sitting-room, dressing-room. Throw a conservatory out over the balcony. Where will you have your rooms?”

“North wing,” said Sir Francis. “Out of reach of Miss Amory’s piano. She screeches from dawn till dusk.”

Strong laughed. “Ah, the musical menace.”

They inspected the kitchen, stables, steward’s room—where Mr. Tatham was poring over estate plans—and finally departed, leaving behind a house that felt more alive than it had in decades.

That night, the news spread: Sir Francis Clavering was back. He was coming to live in the county.

At Chatteris, chaos ensued. High Church, Low Church, half-pay captains, old maids, dowagers, farmers, factory workers—all stirred. The news reached Fairoaks, where Laura said, “Mrs. Pybus says there’s a very pretty girl in the family—Miss Amory, Lady Clavering’s daughter. Of course, you’ll fall in love with her, Arthur.”

Helen scoffed. “Don’t talk nonsense, Laura.”

Pen laughed. “Well, there’s the young Sir Francis.”

“He’s four,” said Laura. “But I shall console myself with that handsome officer—Sir Francis’s friend. He was at church last Sunday. His mustachios were beautiful.”

And so began the legend of Captain Edward Strong—the Chevalier Strong, as he was known abroad. He was tall, broad, blue-eyed, black-whiskered, and carried himself like a man who’d fought wars, survived fireships, and once nearly became king of a small principality in Transylvania.

He’d tried living in the great house, but found it too quiet. “I am a creature born for society,” he told Captain Glanders. “Frank has no energy. He’s not the chest for it.” (And he puffed out his own chest dramatically.)

So he moved into the lodgings formerly occupied by Smirke, charmed Madame Fribsby, dined at the Clavering Arms, joked with farmers, and within days was the most popular man in town. On market day, he knew every girl, every cow, and every secret of the landlord’s strong beer.

He told stories of escaping Missolonghi, being blown up in a fireship, receiving the Cross of the Redeemer, fighting in Spain, and hanging up his sword after Zumalacarreguy’s death. He sang songs in six languages, played the piano like a man possessed, and made Fairoaks feel like a party.

Before long, he was in Helen’s drawing-room as often as he was in Madame Fribsby’s parlor. The ladies adored him. He had tales of Greek captives, Polish beauties, Spanish nuns, and battles fought under moonlight. He was, as Laura put it, “delightful.”

And he didn’t just entertain—he informed. He told them all about the Clavering family, including Miss Amory.

“She paints,” said Strong. “She writes poems. She composes music. She rides like Diana Vernon. She is a muse. A mystery. A femme incomprise.”

“What’s that?” asked Mrs. Pendennis.

Strong smiled. “A woman beyond understanding.”

“I hate clever women,” said Pen.

“Thank you,” said Laura, who was already planning her friendship with Miss Amory.

And so, as the new chapter of Clavering Park unfolded—with furniture arriving in vans, gardens being ignored, and Captain Strong ruling the roost—it seemed clear: the quiet life of Fairoaks was over. The future was loud, dramatic, and possibly involved a piano that would never stop playing.

Every house has its skeleton—usually tucked away in the attic, behind a wardrobe, or beneath a pile of dusty old hats. And while some folks might find comfort in knowing that even the wealthiest and most seemingly blessed neighbors have their secrets, miseries, and occasional bouts of existential dread, others just wish the skeletons would stop whispering so loudly. In Clavering House, the skeleton wasn’t hidden at all—it walked around in velvet slippers, recited poetry in French, and had an uncanny talent for making everyone else feel like they’d failed life’s entrance exam.

Meet Blanche Amory—our dear little Muse, as she liked to call herself. She sang sweetly, spoke with the precision of a poet who’d read too many novels, and possessed such a delicate soul that you could practically hear her heart breaking from three rooms away. On paper, she was a vision: a young lady of genius, sensitivity, and literary ambition. In practice? She was the reason Sir Francis Clavering wanted to throw her down a well.

One afternoon, after a game of billiards and a cigar (a combination known to unlock the deepest secrets), the Baronet turned to Captain Strong and said, “I say, Strong, I wish to the doose your wife was dead.”

“Ah,” replied Strong, puffing thoughtfully, “so do I. But she won’t die. She’ll live forever—just watch. Why do you wish her off the hooks, Frank?”

“Because then you might marry Missy,” drawled Sir Francis. “She ain’t bad-looking. She’ll have ten thousand pounds. That’s a tidy sum for a poor devil like you.”

“And gad, Strong, I hate her worse every day,” the Baronet continued. “I can’t stand her. By gad, I can’t.”

“I wouldn’t take her at twice the price,” laughed Strong. “Never seen such a little devil in my life.”

“I should like to poison her,” declared Sir Francis, with the solemnity of a man who’d just finished reading a particularly tragic novel. “By Jove, I should.”

“Why?” asked Strong. “What has she done now?”

“Nothing particular,” answered the Baronet. “Just her usual tricks. That girl has such a knack for making people miserable it’s almost impressive. Last night she sent the governess crying out of dinner. Then, as I passed Frank’s room, I heard him howling in the dark—his sister had been telling him ghost stories about the haunted house. At lunch, she gave my lady a turn. My wife’s a fool, but she’s a good soul—I’m hanged if she isn’t.”

“What did Missy do?” Strong asked.

“She started talking about the late Amory—my predecessor,” said Sir Francis, grinning. “She pulled out a picture from *The Keepsake* and insisted it looked just like her dear father. Wanted to know where his grave was. Hang her father! Every time Miss Amory brings him up, Lady Clavering bursts into tears. And the little minx does it on purpose—just to spite her mother. Today when she started, I lost my temper. Told her I was her father—and other things. Then, sir, she took a swing at me.”

“And what did she say?” Strong asked, still chuckling.

“Gad, she said I wasn’t her father. That I wasn’t fit to comprehend her. That her father must’ve been a man of genius and fine feelings—whereas I married her mother for money.”

“Well, didn’t you?” asked Strong.

“It don’t make it any pleasanter to hear because it’s true,” sighed Sir Francis. “I ain’t a literary man—but I ain’t the fool she makes me out to be. She always manages to put me in the hole. She turns the whole house upside down with her quiet ways and sentimental airs. I wish she were dead, Ned.”

“It was my wife you wanted dead earlier,” reminded Strong.

“Well,” said the Baronet, with perfect honesty, “when people bore my life out, I do wish they were dead. And I wish Missy were down a well—with all my heart.”

So there you have it. Our charming little Blanche, though gifted with a poetic soul and a mind like a finely tuned harp, had somehow managed to become the emotional black hole of Clavering House. She was intelligent, sensitive, and deeply artistic—qualities that should have made her beloved. Instead, she used them like weapons.

Blanche had been christened Betsy, but promptly renamed herself Blanche—a decision she made with the seriousness of a queen declaring war. Her stepfather, Sir Francis, held one terrifying threat over her: he would call her Betsy in public. The mere mention of it was enough to bring her to heel, like a child afraid of being called “Bessie” at school.

Her education had been rich in emotion and poor in practicality. From an early age, she’d devoured Lamartine, Balzac, George Sand, and every French novel ever written. By sixteen, she’d fallen in love with Prince Rodolph, Prince Djalma, and Indiana—all without leaving the drawing room. She’d settled the divorce question before she’d learned to tie her own shoelaces.

At eleven, she’d felt tender emotions for a Savoyard organ-grinder in Paris whom she believed to be a prince kidnapped by villains. At twelve, she’d been smitten by an old, hideous drawing master—proof, perhaps, that love knows no logic. At thirteen, she’d carried on a secret correspondence with two boys from Monsieur Rogron’s pension, writing letters filled with passion and dramatic declarations that would’ve made Byron blush.

Her heartbreaks were legendary. She’d lost a beloved sister—only to discover later that she was working as a bookkeeper in her father’s grocer shop on the Rue du Bac. Disappointments, estrangements, disillusionments—she collected them like stamps. But she didn’t just suffer; she broadcasted her suffering. If life was unfair, she’d sing an elegy. If someone disappointed her, she’d write a dirge. She was a poet, after all—why shouldn’t she bemoan her lot?

And so, her tears became a daily feature. They flowed like a spring in April—readily available, endlessly renewable. Sentiment, as Horace once observed (though probably not about this), increases with indulgence. The more you cry, the better you get at it. Blanche was a master.

She ruled the household with a soft voice and a sharper tongue. If Lady Clavering said “Sparrowgrass” instead of “Asparagus,” or referred to a “hobject” instead of a “household object,” Missy would correct her with the calm authority of a grammarian from hell. This only made her mother more nervous, which led to more mistakes, which led to more corrections. It was a vicious cycle, and Blanche was the architect.

Only Captain Strong remained immune. His good humor was like a fortress built of optimism and cigars. He could laugh through sarcasm, endure melancholy, and still enjoy a game of billiards. Everyone else, however, lived under Blanche’s poetic tyranny.

Her maid, Pincott, was a woman of gentle spirit and hard work. She came from a family of Parisian colonists who’d seen better days. Her father was crippled, and returning home would only add to the burden. So she stayed, stitching and hemming for Blanche, who demanded new outfits daily, like a fashion editor with a mood disorder.

Pincott was kind, skilled, and utterly exhausted. She rose at dawn, worked till midnight, and was often found weeping into her tea. Blanche, ever the poet, never missed an opportunity to remind her of this:

“I should send you away, Pincott,” she’d say, “you’re too weak, your eyes are failing, and you’re always snivelling. But I keep you because your parents need support. Mind, I go on enduring you for their sake.”

Or: “Pincott, your wretched appearance gives me the migraine. I think I shall make you wear rouge—just to look cheerful.”

Then, after hours of torment, Blanche would go to bed at one o’clock, say, “You may kiss me. Good night. Have the pink dress ready tomorrow,” and fall asleep like a queen.

Meanwhile, Pincott had to rise again at five, already pale and tired, to begin another day of endless alterations. She couldn’t attend Madame Fribsby’s soirées—those delightful evenings of green tea, scandal, and hot Sally-Lunn cakes. Not that anyone missed her. She was invisible, like a shadow in a sunlit room.

But not everyone in the household was ignored. There was Monsieur Alcide Mirobolant, chief of the kitchen, whose flamboyant style made him a local spectacle. On summer afternoons, he’d stroll through Clavering in his light green paletot, crimson velvet waistcoat, blue glass buttons, orange satin neckcloth, and jean boots tipped with shiny leather. He wore a gold-embroidered cap and a gilt cane, believing himself the very image of Parisian elegance.

To the villagers, he looked like a circus performer who’d wandered off course. Children followed him, shouting “Frenchy!” and “Frogs!” Some wanted a lock of his long, flowing hair. Others jeered and hooted. He thought they admired him—until the mob turned ugly.

That’s when Madame Fribsby intervened. She saw the harassed chef and rushed out, hand extended, speaking to him in French. She invited him into her home, stood at the door like a warrior defending her castle, and told the mob they were cowards for bullying a man who couldn’t speak their language.

Mirobolant was moved. He’d never heard French spoken so badly—and yet, he loved it. He vowed she was his guardian angel. The next day, he sent her a basket of delicacies: pineapple cream, lobster mayonnaise, and preserved fruits from Provence, along with a note declaring her kindness had created a “green place in the desert of his existence.”

Their friendship deepened. He called her “La respectable Fribsbi,” “La vertueuse Fribsbi,” and insisted she was his mother. She, meanwhile, remembered a time when someone had spoken to her in French in a very different tone. She glanced up at the portrait of her Carabineer and sighed. At fifty, she still felt like a girl of eighteen.

Alcide, however, refused to see her as anything but a mother figure. When she tried to steer the conversation toward romance, he politely changed the subject. He was devoted—just not in the way she hoped.

And so, the little innocent, Blanche Amory, continued to reign over Clavering House with her tears, her poems, and her unrelenting need to be understood. She was a genius, yes—but also a tyrant, a drama queen, and the reason why Sir Francis Clavering sometimes dreamed of throwing her into a well. And yet, despite everything, she remained, in her own fragile way, a little muse—one who sang sad songs and broke hearts with the grace of a butterfly caught in a net.

Our dear readers have already heard Sir Francis Clavering’s rather blunt assessment of the woman who had handed him both her fortune and a ticket back to England—no small feat, that. And truth be told, the Baronet wasn’t entirely off base. Lady Clavering was not, by any stretch, the most enlightened or polished of ladies. Her education? A couple of years in Europe, in a suburb of London she insisted on calling “Ackney” until her last breath. (She’d been summoned to Calcutta at fifteen, you see, and never quite shook the accent—or the geography.) On her way out east, aboard the *Ramchunder* East Indiaman under Captain Bragg—a man whose name alone could make one shudder—she met her first husband, Mr. Amory, third mate of said vessel. But before we dive into their romance, let’s pause for a moment: Captain Bragg, who was transporting Miss Snell (as she was then) to her father—also a part-owner of the *Ramchunder*—found it necessary to lock up the rebellious rascal of a mate in irons. The ship reached the Cape, where Amory was left behind like an uninvited guest at a funeral. Bragg delivered his charge safely to Calcutta after a voyage marked by storms, near-disasters, and general maritime drama.

Months later, Amory reappeared—having worked his way back from the Cape as a common sailor, married the rich attorney’s daughter against her father’s wishes, tried his hand at indigo planting (failed), became an agent (failed again), edited the *Sunderbund Pilot* (still failed), and quarreled with everyone he met—especially his father-in-law and wife. His career ended in a spectacular crash, forcing him to flee to New South Wales. Along the way, he likely crossed paths with Sir Jasper Rogers, the respected Judge of the Supreme Court of Calcutta—though how much they discussed is anyone’s guess. And here’s the kicker: Amory’s downfall came not from incompetence, but from using his father-in-law’s name—whose handwriting was perfectly legible—to forge letters. The man didn’t need a secretary; he needed a therapist. So when fortune turned her back on Amory, she did so with a flourish.

The European public, bless them, didn’t know half of this. But Mrs. Amory and her father found life in India less than cozy. So, after some thought, it was decided she should return to Europe—with her four-year-old daughter, Betsy (or Blanche, depending on who was counting), and their nurse, Mrs. Bonner, who would later become Lady Clavering’s most trusted confidante. Captain Bragg, ever the gentleman, secured them a house near his own in Pocklington Street.

It was a summer of biblical proportions—rain every day, the kind that makes you want to stay indoors and write poetry about despair. Bragg, naturally, was pompous, disagreeable, and deeply embarrassed by his Indian lodgers. Mrs. Amory, meanwhile, believed the whole of London was whispering about her husband’s ruin—surely the King and Queen were aware! She had a generous allowance from her father, no real reason to stay in England, and so, gratefully, she fled—escaping the shadow of Captain Bragg like a moth from a candle.

Abroad, she was a sensation. People welcomed her everywhere—Paris, Brussels, Milan, Naples, Rome—wherever her fancy led her. She still called Hackney “Ackney,” spoke English with a charming foreign lilt, dressed like a queen, and prepared curries and pilafs at every boarding-house she visited. Her quirks only made her more delightful. She was the most generous, cheerful, and jolly woman alive—bringing triple the champagne to picnics, handing out theater tickets like candy, paying her bills months in advance, and quietly helping poor travelers whose money hadn’t arrived yet. She lived like a whirlwind, leaving trails of kindness and laughter in her wake.

Then came news of Amory’s death—delivered to her in Rome, where Captain Clavering was staying, unable to pay his hotel bill (a shared affliction with his friend, the Chevalier Strong). The good-natured widow, seeing no great loss in her late husband (who, frankly, had been a bit of a disaster), promptly married the descendant of the ancient Clavering line. She professed no deep sorrow—only mild relief. Thus began her reign at Clavering Park, where Mr. Pinckney painted her portrait with her little boy beside her.

Her daughter, Missy, followed her mother’s travels closely and absorbed more life lessons than most girls learn in a lifetime. She had a governess, then Madame de Caramel’s prestigious pension in the Champs Elysées. When the Claverings returned to England, she came too. It wasn’t until her grandfather died and her brother Frank was born that she realized her status had changed. Miss Amory, once nobody’s daughter, now stood in the shadow of Master Francis Clavering, heir to a baronetcy and a vast estate. Had it not been for little Frank, she might have been an heiress herself. But she cared little for money—though she was romantic, dreamy, and full of poetic yearnings. Still, she couldn’t help but resent those who had altered her fate. Her stepfather was dull and weak. Her mother dropped her H’s and lacked refinement. And little Frank—spoiled, quarrelsome, and always stepping on her toes—was a constant irritation. He upset her dinner, trod on her dresses, and seemed to monopolize all affection.

None of them understood her. So her lonely heart sought elsewhere—looking for someone to love, someone to belong to. This dear girl, lacking sympathy at home, grew increasingly restless, irritating her mother and boring her stepfather so thoroughly that they were almost as eager as she was for her to find a husband. Hence Sir Francis’s wish, mentioned earlier, that Mrs. Strong die so he could marry Blanche himself. But since that was impossible, any eligible young man was welcome. And a handsome, well-educated fellow like Arthur Pendennis? He could have proposed with open arms—and Lady Clavering would have welcomed him as a son-in-law, had he shown the courage.

But Mr. Pen, alas, suffered from the peculiar affliction known as self-doubt. He was ashamed of his failures, his idle past, his poverty—which had forced his mother to pinch pennies at Fairoaks. There was vanity in his hesitation, yes, but also genuine remorse. How could he hope to win such a prize? Blanche lived in a grand park, waited on by dozens of servants, while he dined on meager meals brought in by a single maid. The obstacles seemed insurmountable—until he realized they weren’t. But instead of charging forward, he lingered in doubt, perhaps even unaware of what he truly wanted.

Many a young man fails not through lack of merit, but through a species of vanity called shyness. Pen was one of them. He wasn’t certain he loved Blanche—only that he liked her. She was charming, lively, and could charm the birds from the trees. But there were deeper reasons holding him back.

For one thing, his mother saw through her. Mrs. Pendennis sensed something amiss in the girl—light-mindedness, frivolity, a lack of reverence for family and sacred things. Laura, too, began to see flaws beneath the dazzling surface. At first, she defended her new friend fiercely. But over time, she noticed the contradictions. Blanche complained of being misunderstood, yet treated others with cruelty. She lamented her mother’s ignorance, yet showed no patience. She criticized Sir Francis for being unintellectual, yet demanded everything be done her way. She spoke tenderly of her brother, calling him “my pretty baby sweet,” promising to love him always, even if the world proved false—but then boxed his ears when he spilled water on her drawing.

Laura, with her gentle heart, couldn’t help but recall the touching verses Blanche had read from *Mes Larmes*, where she vowed eternal devotion to her brother. And now, here she was, treating him like a servant. It was a cruel lesson in hypocrisy—one Laura had seen before, though not in such a vivid form. For Pen, too, had flaws—waywardness, selfishness—but beneath them beat a kind heart. And now, watching Blanche, Laura felt the sting of disillusionment. Her admiration faded, replaced by a quiet sense of superiority. She began to speak to Blanche with calm dignity, a tone the Muse disliked intensely.

Nobody likes to be exposed. And when you’ve been worshipped, stepping down feels like falling. Blanche, sensing this shift, grew peevish, irritable, and worse. One fateful day, she was particularly awful—insolent to her mother, savage with little Frank, cruel to her attendant Pincott, whom she tormented like a spoiled child with a cat. Poor Pincott, driven from the room in tears, received a final sneer as she fled. That was the straw that broke the camel’s back.

Laura exploded. She couldn’t believe how someone so young could be so rude, so unkind. She accused Blanche of wickedness, declared her conduct sinful, and stormed out—leaving Blanche stunned and humiliated. Back at Fairoaks, she confessed to her mother: “Mamma, you were right. Blanche is selfish, cruel. She speaks of love, but has no heart. No honest girl would treat a mother so, or torment a servant. I give her up. From this day, I will have no other friend but you.”

They embraced—long, tearful, heartfelt. Mrs. Pendennis felt a secret joy. Her daughter’s rejection meant Pen would never marry Blanche. He would see her true nature soon enough. He’d be saved from a flighty creature, awakened from a dream.

But Laura didn’t tell her mother the real cause of the quarrel. Earlier that day, Blanche had been in a mischievous mood. She’d brought up Mr. Pen—how he’d once been involved with an actress, how he’d been “false.” Laura, blushing, defended him: “He was generous. She was the one who was untrue. He nearly broke his heart.” Blanche, archly, asked if he’d written her poems. Laura admitted only to reading two: *The Fisherman and the Diver*, and his Prize Poem—“pompous and prosy,” she laughed. Then Blanche, with a sly smile, whispered, “Promise not to tell anyone, and I’ll show you something.”

She fetched a mother-of-pearl desk, opened it with a silver key, and pulled out crumpled papers stained with green ink. Love poems—about Undine, about Naiads, about rivers. Laura read them, but her eyes blurred. “Have you answered them?” she asked. “No, not for worlds,” Blanche replied, slipping them back. Then she sang Rossini songs with perfect trills, while Laura sat silently, lost in thought.

After lunch, they walked arm-in-arm down the corridor. Just before entering the hall, Laura paused, looked at her friend kindly, and kissed her with sisterly warmth. Something—perhaps Frank’s manners, or mamma’s mistakes—set Blanche off again. And thus began the storm.

So ends the tale of a lady who danced through life with charm and grace, only to reveal herself as a mortal, flawed, and very human. And of a young man who hesitated, not because he was unworthy, but because he was afraid to be happy.

The feud between the two girls didn’t last long—mostly because Laura was too eager to forgive, and Blanche’s grudge had never been serious in the first place. Nobody really minds being called wicked; it doesn’t scratch the ego. In fact, Blanche found her friend’s outrage oddly flattering—like a compliment disguised as an accusation. And since they both knew the real reason behind the drama (though neither would say a word), the tension melted away like sugar in tea.

So Laura sighed and admitted that the romantic spark of their friendship had fizzled out—and that Blanche was now just someone worth ordinary regard. As for Blanche, she immediately penned a heart-wrenching poem about betrayal and unrequited devotion. It was the classic tale: love met with indifference, loyalty returned with neglect. She even found a new confidante among the daughters of some London newcomers who’d recently arrived at Clavering Park. Miss Amory, ever the social climber, took this young lady under her wing and poured out all her sorrows into her eager ears.

Meanwhile, the tall footmen stopped coming to Fairoaks with notes for sweet Laura, and the pony-carriage barely left the drive anymore. When Laura did visit, Blanche would adopt a look of tragic martyrdom—eyes downcast, lips trembling—while Laura just laughed and treated the whole performance like a particularly silly play.

But if Blanche found solace in female friendships, she also discovered male companionship that seemed to comfort her just as well. If ever she chatted with a young man—whether strolling through a garden, leaning out a drawing-room window, or pausing mid-waltz—she’d turn on the charm: eyes wide, voice soft, tone full of tender appeal. Then, like clockwork, she’d vanish, leaving the poor fellow to replay the entire scene in his head—just so he could do it again with the next suitor.

When the Claverings first arrived at the Park, there weren’t many eligible men around. Pen had the field all to himself. In Clavering town? No young men. Nearby? Just a few clergymen and country squires with ill-fitting coats and feet like trunks. The Baronet didn’t invite the dragoons stationed at Chatteris—his own regiment, no less—and had left on bad terms over a horse deal, a disputed gambling account, and a scandal involving a white feather and blind-Hookey. But let’s not dwell on the past unless it helps move the story forward.

Then came autumn—the end of Parliament, the London season, and a wave of county families descending upon their ancestral homes. Baymouth, the nearby seaside resort, buzzed with activity. Mr. Bingley’s Theatre Royal in Chatteris reopened. The Assizes and Race Balls drew their usual crowds. And finally, after months of polite distance, the great families began to call.

The Earl of Trehawk, Lord Lieutenant of the County, arrived at Eyrie Castle. The Countess Dowager of Rockminster, whose son was a local magnate, settled into a mansion on the Marine Parade. They made their grand entrance at Clavering Park, and suddenly every noble family in the region followed suit—carriages rolling down the avenue like a parade of peacocks.

And thus began Mirobolant’s golden age—when art could distract him from the agony of unrequited love. The footmen were too busy delivering messages to be seen near Fairoaks. Blanche had new friends, new walks, new places to avoid Pen’s fishing rod. He came every day, casting lines into the stream, but the fish refused to bite, and the Peri—well, she wasn’t appearing.

Now, in strict confidence (and please don’t tell anyone), we must mention the hollow tree. You may recall Pen used it during his romance with Miss Fotheringay. Later, he repurposed it—not for fishing gear, but as a secret post office. Under moss and a flat stone, he tucked little poems and letters addressed to an Undine or Naiad who supposedly haunted the river. Occasionally, he’d find a flower in return—or a note in delicate handwriting, in French or English, written on pink scented paper.

Yes, Miss Amory did walk by that stream. Yes, she did use pink scented paper. But after the aristocrats descended upon Clavering Park, nobody came to collect Pen’s letters. The white notes stayed under the stone, untouched. The tree reflected in the water. The stream rolled on.

There wasn’t much in those letters—just a few words, half-joking, half-sympathetic, the kind any young woman might write. But oh, you foolish Pendennis—if you wanted her, why didn’t you *say* so? Maybe neither of you meant it seriously. Maybe you were just playing at love, and she was playing along.

But when a man is shut out of a game he thought he was winning, he often gets angry. Pen began to take his poems very seriously. He felt tragically romantic again—like in his first crush. He decided he needed answers.

One day he went to the Hall and found a room full of visitors. Another time, Miss Amory was resting—preparing for a ball. Pen cursed balls, cursed his lack of money, cursed his lowly position in the county that kept him off the guest lists. On a third attempt, he found her in the garden—but walking with none other than the Bishop and Bishopess of Chatteris, who glared at him like he’d stolen their communion wine. The Bishop remembered him. Remembered the incident in the Dean’s garden.

“The Bishop says you’re a sad young man,” Lady Clavering whispered kindly. “What have you done? Nothing, I hope, to vex your dear Mar? How is she? Why doesn’t she come see us? We haven’t seen her in ages. We’ve been going about, gadding, so we don’t see neighbors. Give my love to her and Laura. Come to dinner tomorrow.”

Mrs. Pendennis was too ill to attend, but Laura and Pen came. There was a large party. Pen managed a brief word with Miss Amory.

“You never come to the river now,” he said.

“I can’t,” she replied. “The house is full of people.”

“Undine has left the stream,” Pen said, trying to sound poetic.

“She never should have gone there,” Miss Amory answered. “She won’t go again. It was foolish. Wrong. Only play. Besides,” she added, looking him straight in the eye before dropping her gaze, “you have other consolations at home.”

If he wanted her, why didn’t he speak? She might have said yes. But when she mentioned “other consolations,” he thought of Laura—so kind, so pure—and of his mother, who had hoped so desperately to unite him with her adopted daughter.

“Blanche!” he began, irritated.

“Miss Amory!”

“Laura is looking at us, Mr. Pendennis,” she said. “I must go back.” And she ran off, leaving Pen to chew his nails and stare into the moonlight.

Laura *was* watching. She was talking with Mr. Pynsent, Lord Rockminster’s son—grandson of the Dowager Lady—seated in state, receiving Lady Clavering’s awkward grammar and patronizing Sir Francis. Pynsent and Pen had once been classmates at Oxbridge, where Pen had been the more fashionable, perhaps even a bit arrogant toward the younger gentleman. They’d met again today and exchanged that peculiar English semi-nod—the one that says, “You’re here? Well, I suppose I’ll pretend to recognize you.”

“I knew that man at Oxbridge,” Mr. Pynsent said to Miss Bell. “A Mr. Pendennis, I think.”

“Yes,” she said.

“He seems rather sweet on Miss Amory,” he continued.

Laura looked at them. Perhaps she thought the same. But she said nothing.

“A man of large property in the county, isn’t he?” asked Pynsent. “He used to talk about representing it. Whereabouts are his estates?”

Laura smiled. “His estates lie on the other side of the river, near the lodge-gate. He’s my cousin. I live there.”

“Where?” Pynsent asked, laughing.

“On the other side of the river—at Fairoaks,” she answered.

“Many pheasants there? Cover looks good,” he said.

Laura smiled again. “We have nine hens and a cock, a pig, and an old pointer.”

“Pendennis doesn’t preserve, then?” Pynsent asked.

“You should come and see him,” Laura said, laughing. She was amused by the idea that her Pen was a great landowner.

“Indeed, I quite long to renew our acquaintance,” Pynsent said gallantly, his eyes saying clearly, “It’s *you* I want to see.” To which Laura gave a small smile and a graceful bow.

Then Blanche arrived, flashing her most dazzling smile, and begged Laura to join her for a song. Laura, always obliging, went to the piano. Pynsent listened politely until Miss Amory began singing, then walked away.

“What a nice, frank, amiable, well-bred girl that is, Wagg,” Pynsent said to his companion. “The tall one—ringlets, red lips—monstrous red, aren’t they?”

“What do you think of the girl of the house?” Wagg asked.

“I think she’s a lean, scraggy humbug,” Pynsent replied. “She drags her shoulders out of her dress. Never leaves her eyes alone. Simpers and ogles like a French maid.”

“Pynsent, be civil,” the other man said. “Someone might hear.”

“Oh, it’s Pendennis of Boniface,” Pynsent said. “Fine evening, Mr. Pendennis—we were just talking of your charming cousin.”

“Any relation to Major Pendennis?” Wagg asked.

“His nephew,” Pen replied, offering his best manner. “Had the pleasure of meeting you at Gaunt House.” Instantly, the acquaintanceship was sealed.

The next afternoon, Pen returned from a fruitless fishing trip to find the two gentlemen seated in his mother’s drawing room—chatting comfortably with the widow and her ward.

Pynsent, tall and gaunt with red whiskers and a prominent chin tuft, was perched on a chair near Miss Laura. She was enjoying his conversation—simple, direct, slightly humorous, laced with slang. It was the first time she’d seen a true London dandy. Back when Mr. Foker visited, she’d been just a child, and he’d been more boy than man.

Wagg, meanwhile, observed everything. “Old gardener,” he muttered, seeing John at the lodge. “Red livery waistcoat—clothes drying on the gooseberry bush—blue aprons, white ducks—gad, they must be Pendennis’s ducks. Nobody else wears ’em. Shy place for a rising politician, eh, Pynsent?”

“Snug little crib,” Pynsent said. “Pretty cosy lawn.”

“Mr. Pendennis at home, old man?” Wagg asked.

“No, Master Pendennis is agone out,” John replied.

“Are the ladies at home?” the younger visitor asked.

“Yes, they be,” John said. As they walked up the gravel path, Wagg noted every detail: the barometer, the letter bag, the umbrellas, the clogs, Pen’s hats, the tartan wrapper. Old John opened the door. Wagg took it all in.

“Old fellow does all the work,” he whispered. “Caleb Balderstone. Shouldn’t wonder if he’s the housemaid.”

They entered the drawing room. Pynsent recognized two perfectly well-bred women. Wagg bowed extravagantly, with florid gestures and knowing winks. Pynsent ignored the signals, choosing instead to be haughty toward Wagg and deferential to the ladies.

To Wagg, poverty was comedy. He had the soul of a butler who’d been promoted to the drawing room. His jokes were endless, his good nature genuine—but he couldn’t grasp that a gentleman might wear an old coat, or that a lady could be respectable without a carriage or a French milliner.

“Charming place, ma’am,” he said, bowing to the widow. “Noble prospect—delightful for us Cocknies, who seldom see anything but Pall Mall.”

The widow simply said she’d never been to London except once—before her son was born.

“Fine village, ma'am, fine village,” Wagg continued. “Increasing every day. It’ll be a large town soon. Not a bad place for those who can’t get to the country. Will repay a visit.”

“My brother, Major Pendennis, has often mentioned your name,” the widow said. “And we’ve enjoyed some of your droll books, sir,” Helen added—though she hated them.

“He is my very good friend,” Wagg said, bowing low. “One of the best-known men about town. Where known, appreciated—I assure you, appreciated. He’s with Steyne in Aix-la-Chapelle. Steyne has the gout. So does your brother. I’m going to Stillbrook for pheasant-shooting, then Bareacres—where I’ll likely meet Pendennis.” He launched into a torrent of fashionable chatter, naming peers and rattling on breathlessly. The widow listened in stunned silence. *What a man,* she thought. *Are all London gentlemen like this? Pen will never like him.*

Pynsent, meanwhile, was chatting with Laura. He named houses he planned to visit, hoped to see her at them, suggested she spend a season in London, hinted he might canvass the county in the next election, and asked for Pendennis’s support. He praised Pen’s oratory at Oxbridge, asked if he was entering politics too. He spoke pleasantly, charmingly—until Pen returned.

Pen greeted them courteously. Though he remembered a debate at Oxbridge where he’d declared his intention to enter Parliament and delivered a grand speech thanking everyone for electing him—he didn’t hold it against Pynsent. The young man was so open and friendly that Pen hoped he’d forgotten the boastful moment.

Pen adjusted his tone, talked about Plinlimmon, Magnus Charters, the old Oxford set—with easy familiarity, as if he lived among dukes every day.

Then, at six o’clock, Betsy the maid burst in—door flung wide, tray in hand—carrying three tea cups, a teapot, and a plate of thick bread-and-butter.

All of Pen’s dignity vanished. He froze. “What will they think?” he thought.

Wagg stuck his tongue in his cheek, found the tea pathetic, and winked at Pynsent.

But Pynsent saw nothing wrong. People drank tea at six if they wanted to. He later asked Wagg, “What the devil were you grinning at?”

“Didn’t you see how the cub was ashamed of the thick bread-and-butter? I bet they’re having treacle if they’re good. I’ll tell old Pendennis when we get back to town.”
