“Don’t see the fun,” Pynsent said.

“Never thought you would,” Wagg growled. They walked home in silence.

At dinner, Wagg recounted the visit with hilarious precision. He described old John, the clothes drying, the clogs, the drawing room, the pictures: “Old man with a beak and bald head—feu Pendennis, I bet two to one. Sticking-plaster portrait of a youth in cap and gown—the present Marquis of Fairoaks, of course. The widow in miniature—Mrs. Mee. She wore that dress when we came, or one made the year after. Tips cut off her gloves—she stitches her son’s collars with them. Then the servant came in with tea, so we left the Earl and Countess to their bread-and-butter.”

Blanche, sitting beside him, adored witty men and burst out laughing. “You’re such an odd, droll creature!”

Pynsent, however, was disgusted. “I don’t know what sort of ladies you’re used to, Mr. Wagg,” he said loudly, “but I never met two better-bred women in my life. And I hope, ma'am, you’ll call upon them,” he added, turning to Lady Rockminster.

Sir Francis whispered to his neighbor: “That’s what I call a sticker for Wagg.”

Lady Clavering tapped Pynsent with her fan, winked, and said, “Mr. Pynsent, you’re a good feller.”

After the Blanche affair, a subtle change passed over Laura’s conversation with Pen. A hint of melancholy, a touch of bitterness. She seemed to examine him—and find him lacking. Her mother noticed the way her clear eyes watched him, the look of almost scorn when he lounged with the women, smoked lazily on the lawn, or lay under a tree reading a book he wasn’t reading.

“What’s happened?” Helen asked. “Something has. Has that wicked little Blanche been meddling?”

“Nothing has happened,” Laura said.

“Then why do you look at Pen so?” the widow asked sharply.

“Look at him, dear mother?” the girl replied. “We two women are no society for him. We don’t interest him. We’re not clever enough for a genius like Pen. He wastes his life among us, tied to our apron-strings. He cares for nothing. Won’t go beyond the garden gate. Even Captain Glanders and Captain Strong bore him,” she added with a bitter laugh. “And they’re men, you know—our superiors. He’ll never be happy here. Why, is he not facing the world? Without a profession?”

“We have enough,” the widow said, her heart pounding. “Pen hasn’t spent a penny in months. He’s very good. He could be happy with us.”

“Don’t distress yourself, dear mother,” Laura said gently. “I don’t like to see you upset. Don’t be sad because Pen is unhappy. All men are. They must work. They must make names for themselves. Look—those captains fought battles. Mr. Pynsent is rich and works hard. He wants a reputation. He said Pen was one of the best speakers at Oxbridge. Pen laughs at Wagg’s fame—and calls him a dunce. Says anyone could write his books.”

“I’m sure they’re vulgar,” the widow interjected.

And so the chapter ends—quietly, like a sigh in the evening air.

In the dim, calico-draped nook of a window at Clavering Park, Arthur Pendennis adopted the solemn expression of a man who’d just discovered he’d lost his last shilling—and perhaps his soul. There he stood, like a disgruntled statue, watching Miss Laura Bell waltz through her first quadrille with Mr. Pynsent as partner. Now, Mr. Pynsent was not merely serious—he was *gravely* serious. He danced as if attending a funeral in church, each step measured, each glance directed to the heavens above, as though awaiting divine approval for his footwork. Not a smile marred his face; not a twitch betrayed his inner life. He was a man whose dance moves were likely rehearsed in silence, by candlelight, while reading Milton.

But poor Laura! She was all joy, all light, all unspoiled country charm. Her white dress flared out like a daisy in bloom, her brown curls tumbled over her shoulders like a waterfall of chocolate, and her rosy cheeks glowed as if she’d been kissed by the sun itself. The music, the lights, the sheer madness of it all—she devoured it. And so did the room. More than one gentleman, from the most discreet to the most absurdly bearded, found themselves doing double takes at her. Lady Fogey, who had a house in London and thought that gave her the right to judge anything (especially other people’s faces), leaned over to Lady Rockminster and whispered, “Who is that young person?” Then, after a moment’s study, declared: “She resembles the reigning beauty of the season—though not quite as tall—but she’ll do.”

Lady Rockminster nearly choked on her fan. “Do? My dear, I’ve never seen a girl who wouldn’t ‘do’!” she snapped. “And what gives you the audacity to assess such matters at all?”

She then examined Laura with the intensity of a royal inspector surveying a new breed of spaniel. “Her manner is excellent,” she decided. “Her arms are rather red, but that’s youth, my dear. And her tone—oh, far superior to that little minx Miss Amory, dancing opposite.”

Indeed, Miss Blanche Amory was currently twirling across the floor like a miniature tornado in silk. She was, of course, Laura’s vis-à-vis, which meant they passed each other every few seconds like two rival planets in a solar system of social ambition. Blanche smiled at Laura with a sweetness that could only be described as patronizing. She nodded, she murmured, she winked—always making sure Laura knew she was being observed. Her shoulders—white as alabaster—never stopped shifting, as if they feared being caught still. Her eyes darted about like nervous sparrows. Her whole body seemed to shout: “Look at me! Look at my tiny foot! Look at my exquisite shoe! This is Parisian elegance! Not that rustic, bouncing country lass over there—Miss Bell, who couldn’t even step in time until *I* taught her!”

Laura danced on, blissfully unaware of the war being waged around her. But she *did* keep an eye on Pen behind the curtain. He didn’t move during the first quadrille. Not a muscle. Not a blink. He remained frozen like a particularly bitter statue—until Lady Clavering, bless her good-natured soul, beckoned him up to the dais where the dowagers sat like ancient queens judging the world. Pen blushed, tripped slightly, and made his way forward with the grace of a man trying to walk on ice while wearing boots full of peas.

He bowed stiffly to Lady Rockminster, who barely glanced at him. Then he turned to the widow of the late Amory—a woman who looked like she’d been dressed by a jeweler with a grudge against modesty—decked in diamonds, lace, feathers, and enough gold to buy a small nation. He offered his respects, she accepted them with a nod, and he retreated to the shadows of conversation.

Young Mr. Fogey, then in the fifth form at Eton and already dreaming of spurs and dragoon commissions, stepped up next to dance with Miss Bell. He was smitten. Utterly, completely, hopelessly. “You’re much more charming than the French girl,” he said honestly, referring to Miss Amory, whom he’d just danced with.

Laura laughed—brightly, warmly, like a bell rung in spring. And as she laughed, she caught sight of Pen. And Pen, in turn, stared back with the intensity of a man who’d just realized he’d left the front door open.

The next dance was a waltz. Young Fogey sighed dramatically. “I don’t know how to waltz,” he confessed. “I shall have a master next holidays.”

Mr. Pynsent claimed Laura again. Pen watched, furious. How dare she be twirled round by *him*? It was unthinkable! Back in the summer evenings at Clavering Park, when the tables were cleared and the governess played piano, Pendennis and the Chevalier Strong—whose dance repertoire included British hornpipes, German waltzes, and Spanish fandangos—had often improvised little balls with the girls. Laura had always been the spark of those dances. Even Mr. Pynsent, usually as stiff as a poker, had once or twice loosened up under her infectious energy.

But now, Blanche had a problem. Her partner, Captain Broadfoot of the Dragoons, was a disaster. He tried hard—very hard—but lacked rhythm, musicality, and any sense of timing. His movements were slow, ponderous, like a rhinoceros learning to tap-dance. So, naturally, Blanche saw Laura stealing the spotlight—and wasn’t pleased.

After a few turns with the heavy-handed captain, she feigned fatigue and asked to be led back to her mother. “Why didn’t you ask me to waltz?” she demanded of Pen. “Why leave me to the mercy of that odious man in spurs and a red coat?”

“I thought spurs and scarlet were the most fascinating things in the world to young ladies,” Pen replied, smirking. “I never would have dared to put my black coat in competition with that splendid red jacket.”

“You’re cruel and sulky and naughty,” Blanche declared, shrugging. “You’d better go away. Your cousin is watching us over Mr. Pynsent’s shoulder.”

“Will you waltz with me?” Pen asked.

“No,” she said firmly. “Not this waltz. I just sent away Captain Broadfoot. Look at Mr. Pynsent—have you ever seen such a creature? But I’ll dance the next waltz with you. And the quadrille too. I’m promised, but I’ll tell Mr. Poole I forgot.”

“Women forget very easily,” Pendennis said.

“But they always come back,” Blanche countered, “repentant and sorry.”

Just then, Laura arrived, leaning on Mr. Pynsent like a bookshelf holding up a pile of novels. “I’m so glad to have come, dear Pen,” she said, offering her hand. “How is mamma? The three dances are over. I’m engaged to you for the next one.”

“I’ve just promised to dance with Miss Amory,” Pen said.

Blanche nodded curtly. “I don’t intend to give him up, dearest Laura.”

“Well, then,” Laura said sweetly, “he’ll waltz with me, dear Blanche. Won’t you, Pen?”

“I promised Miss Amory,” Pen repeated.

“Provoking!” Laura exclaimed, curtsied, and moved off to take refuge under Lady Rockminster’s wing.

Pen, meanwhile, felt triumphant. The two prettiest girls in the room were fighting over him. He leaned against the wall, elbow propped, and began to mock the entire gathering—dragoons, dandies, and ladies with hair so elaborate it looked like someone had thrown a fruit basket into their coiffure.

“One has a bird’s nest on her head,” he quipped. “Another has six pounds of grapes—plus false pearls. It’s a coiffure of almonds and raisins,” he added. “Could be served for dessert.”

He was sharp, witty, and utterly merciless. As the quadrille progressed, he kept Blanche laughing—not just at his jokes, but at the fact that Laura could see them, hear them, and feel excluded. “Arthur is charming tonight,” Blanche whispered to Laura.

“Who?” Laura asked.

“Arthur,” Blanche replied, switching to French. “Oh, what a pretty name!”

Then the young ladies joined Pen’s side, and Cornet Perch performed a pas seul. He had no waistcoat pockets, so his hands hung awkwardly—large, swollen, like two sausages tied to his sleeves.

Between dances, Pen ignored Laura—except to ask if Cornet Perch was amusing, and whether she liked him better than Mr. Pynsent. Two subtle daggers to the heart. Then he returned to Blanche, joking loudly, proudly, like a man who knew he was winning.

Laura, confused and hurt, couldn’t understand why Pen was so cold. But she wasn’t angry—she was too kind-hearted for that. And let’s face it: a man’s jealousy, even when poorly executed, can be oddly flattering.

So when Pen refused to dance with her, she took the Chevalier Strong instead. And since she loved dancing—like any sensible, lively young woman should—she threw herself into it with gusto.

Captain Broadfoot, meanwhile, was paired with Miss Roundle, a lady of proportions so vast that she might have been sculpted by a baker. She wore a strawberry-ice-colored crape dress, and together they moved like a pair of slow-moving glaciers.

Now, Pen—armed with Blanche and a sense of superiority—set off on his waltzing triumph. He spun, he whirled, he felt brilliant. He may have glanced toward Laura to see if she approved. But she was busy with Strong, oblivious.

But glory is fleeting. Just as Pen and Blanche reached their peak of elegance, they collided—full force—with Captain Broadfoot and Miss Roundle. The result? A catastrophic chain reaction. All four went down. Pen landed flat on his back. Blanche bounced onto a bench. Miss Roundle collapsed like a deflated balloon. Captain Broadfoot laughed—good-naturedly, as if it were all part of the fun.

But Blanche was furious. She was humiliated. And when the crowd giggled, Pen turned crimson. He hated laughter—at least, laughter aimed at him. He stormed over to apologize, blaming the pink-clad woman, asking if she was hurt, offering another dance.

She refused. She was *very* hurt. A cavalier with a blue ribbon and a three-pointed star rushed forward with a glass of water. She drank it gracefully, smiled at him, then turned her white shoulder toward Pen with the hauteur of a queen dismissing a servant. She held out her hand.

The man trembled. He bowed. He pressed her hand to his chest. He was ecstatic.

It was Mirobolant—the cook.

Pen, stunned, cried out: “By Jove, it’s the cook!”

The words slipped out before he could stop them. He instantly regretted it. Because Blanche had invited Mirobolant to escort her. And Mirobolant, hearing the insult, shot Pen a look that could have melted steel.

The artist, poor fellow, had never considered himself less than anyone else. In his native land, he’d seen noblewomen dance with peasants. He would have danced with Blanche himself—if not for Pen’s cruel remark. But Strong, knowing the truth, stepped in. He muttered something in rapid Spanish, and urged Blanche to take an ice.

Mirobolant, crestfallen, bowed deeply and withdrew.

“Don’t you know who he is?” Strong asked.

“The chef Mirobolant.”

“How should I know?” Blanche replied. “He has a croix. He’s distinguished. He has beautiful eyes.”

“The poor fellow is mad for your beaux yeux,” Strong said. “He’s a good cook, but not quite right in the head.”

“What did you say to him?” Blanche asked.

“He’s a Gascon, from the borders of Spain. I told him he’d lose his job if he walked with you.”

“Poor Monsieur Mirobolant!” Blanche sighed. “Did you see the look he gave Pendennis? I think he’d like to run him through with one of his spits.”

“Broadfoot looked like he’d like to kill him too,” Strong said. “So did Pynsent.”

“What ice will you have?” he asked. “Water ice or cream ice?”

“Water ice. Who is that odd man staring at me? He’s decore too.”

“That’s Colonel Altamont,” Strong said. “A very queer character. Serves the Nawaub of Lucknow.”

“Hallo! What’s that noise?” Strong suddenly shouted, and dashed out to investigate.

The refreshment room—reserved for the elite, guarded by a waiter who barred entry to commoners—was now occupied by the Colonel, who had been drinking champagne steadily all night. He was flushed, slightly unsteady, and had taken a peculiar interest in everyone who entered.

When Strong left, the Colonel rose, eyes glowing like embers, and approached Blanche, who was sipping her ice.

“Who was he?” she asked later. “It was quite exciting.”

“And so you’re Betsy Amory,” he said, gazing at her.

“Betsy Amory, by Jove!”

“Who—who speaks to me?” she stammered.

But the noise in the ballroom was growing louder. We must rush back—before the chaos reaches its climax.

The ballroom at Baymouth was in full swing—music, laughter, the occasional cry of “He’s got her!” from a gentleman who’d just managed to catch his partner before she fell into the punch bowl. But then, disaster struck. A minor war broke out near the window, where the air was thick with sarcasm, ill-temper, and one very offended French chef.

Outside, the street crowd—probably bored with their own lives—cheered on the drama like it was a boxing match. “Pitch him in!” they yelled. “Where’s the police? Or better yet, *where’s the fire brigade*?” Inside, two factions had formed: one around Monsieur Alcide Mirobolant, the Chevalier de Juillet (which, if you’re not careful, sounds like a brand of fancy vinegar), and the other around Arthur Pendennis, who was currently trying not to lose his temper, his dignity, or possibly his footing.

And then, like a knight riding in on a donkey with a horn, Captain Strong burst through the door. He didn’t so much enter as *shoulder* his way in, nearly knocking over Madame Fribsby, who shrieked, “Save him! Save him!”—as if he were about to be sacrificed to an ancient god of bad manners.

Now, what caused this ruckus? Let’s rewind. Mr. Pen, still smarting from his recent tumble during a waltz (a fall that had made him look like a startled badger caught mid-dance), was attempting to regain composure by gazing out the window at the sea. The ocean, sparkling under the moonlight, seemed to say, “Hey, buddy, chill out.” But just as Pen was about to whisper, “You’re right,” a hand landed on his shoulder.

He turned—and there stood Mirobolant, eyes wide, face pale, hair like a stormy night, and clearly furious. To be tapped on the shoulder by a French cook? That was worse than being called “sir” by someone who meant “you’re fired.”

“You speak French?” asked Mirobolant, in fluent, dramatic French.

“What’s it to you?” Pen replied in English, because he was not going to play along with any foreign language games unless they involved romance or a duel.

“Ah, but you understand it?” Mirobolant pressed, bowing with theatrical flair.

“Yes,” said Pen, stamping his foot. “I understand it *very* well.”

“Then listen carefully, Monsieur Pendennis,” Mirobolant declared, rolling his Rs like a cannonball down a hill. “Vous êtes un lâche. A coward. A *lache*. Do you know what that means?”

Pen blinked. “Yes, I do. It means you’re about to get punched.”

“No,” said Mirobolant, putting a hand on his hip like a man who had seen many duels and lost none. “It means *you are a coward*, and therefore must fight me.”

“I’ll fling you out the window,” bellowed Pen, lunging forward. “You impudent little *coq*!”

At which point, Captain Broadfoot (who looked like he’d been born to stop fights) and another officer jumped between them. The ladies screamed. The fiddles stopped. Laura, looking like she’d just witnessed a tragedy in a soap opera, leaned over the crowd and whispered, “What in Heaven’s name is happening?”

Strong arrived just in time—like a divine intervention dressed in a tailcoat and a slightly crooked hat. He found Mirobolant grinding his teeth and muttering in a language that sounded suspiciously like French with extra rage.

“What happened?” Strong asked, speaking in Spanish, probably to avoid confusion.

“I am Chevalier de Juillet,” Mirobolant announced, slapping his chest like a man who’d once fought Napoleon personally. “And he insulted me.”

“What did he say?” Strong asked.

“He called me… *Cuisinier*,” hissed the chef, as if the word alone could cause national trauma.

Strong tried not to laugh. “Come, poor Chevalier,” he said, leading him away. “We can’t have duels in front of ladies. And frankly, the poor fellow’s not right in the head.” He whispered this to the crowd, and everyone nodded sagely—especially Laura, whose face lit up with concern.

Pen, meanwhile, was being interrogated by a group of concerned onlookers.

“I don’t know,” he said, puffing his chest. “He was offering his arm to a young lady, so I told him he was a cook. He called me a coward and challenged me. I was so shocked I nearly threw him out the window—hadn’t you all stepped in?”

The gentlemen around him nodded gravely. “D—— him,” one said. “Serve him right. Foreigners! Always thinking they’re better than us. Should’ve been thrown out *first*.”

“But—I’m sorry if I hurt his feelings,” Pen added, suddenly sounding less like a hero and more like a man who’d just realized he’d accidentally insulted a minor nobleman.

Laura rushed to his side. “You’ll shake hands with him before you go to sleep, won’t you, Pen?” she asked, eyes soft as butter. “Foreigners are sensitive. They have different customs. If you hurt someone’s feelings, you should apologize.”

She looked like an angel—maybe even a saint. Pen took her hands, stared into her kind eyes, and said yes, he would.

“How fond that girl is of me,” he thought, smugly. “Should I tell her now? No. Not until I’ve settled this business with the cook.”

Laura, ever the persistent one, asked if he wouldn’t stay and dance with her. “I’m not afraid to waltz with you,” she said, sweetly.

This was a mistake.

Pen immediately saw himself falling again—this time, dramatically, into Miss Roundle, sending her crashing into the wall, while Pynsent laughed. “I shall never dance again,” he declared, darkly. “Never. I’m surprised you’d ask me.”

“Is it because you can’t get Blanche?” Laura asked, with a mischievous glint in her eye.

“No,” Pen snapped. “Because I don’t want to make a fool of myself. For *you* to laugh at me, Laura. I saw you and Pynsent. By Jove! No man shall laugh at me.”

“Pen, Pen, don’t be so wicked!” she cried, wounded by his sudden fury. “Who thinks worse of you for stumbling? Not me. Not anyone.”

Just then, Pynsent appeared. “Lady Rockminster sends her regards,” he said smoothly. “May I escort you to supper?”

“I—I was going with my cousin,” Laura said.

“Oh, pray, no,” Pen interrupted. “You’re in good hands. I’ll leave you. I’m going home.”

“Goodnight, Mr. Pendennis,” Pynsent said, dry as dust—a clear signal that he wanted to box Pen’s ears and call him “pompous idiot.”

Pen didn’t reply. He just bowed and left, ignoring Laura’s pleading eyes.

As soon as he was gone, Pynsent sighed. “What a lovely night,” he said. “The sea is murmuring like a contented cat. I’d rather be walking on the beach than in this stifling room.”

“Very,” said Laura.

Pynsent continued. “Such a strange mix of people. I’ve had to charm the attorney’s daughters, the apothecary’s wife, and a man in the refreshment-room who insisted on buying me champagne. He looked like a pirate who’d just robbed a ship and was trying to impress the locals.”

Laura glanced at him. “I wasn’t listening. I was worried about your cousin and that… that French person.”

“Your cousin has been having a rough night,” Pynsent said. “He hasn’t pleased anyone—Captain Broadwood, the officer, the girl in red, Blanche, the cook, and… me.”

“Didn’t he leave me in your care?” Laura asked, blushing.

“Indeed, I forgive him for that,” Pynsent said warmly. And off they went to supper, Laura clinging to his arm like a prize she’d won in a game show.

But Laura wasn’t hungry. She was distracted. She couldn’t stop thinking about Pen—his jealousy, his anger, his ridiculous pride. How could her mother possibly expect her to marry such a man? And if she did, would it make him happy?

Half an hour later, a waiter handed her a note in pencil:

> *I met Cooky below, ready to fight. I asked his pardon. I’m glad I did. I wanted to speak to you tonight, but will wait till you come home. God bless you. Dance all night with Pynsent and be happy. —PEN.*

Laura smiled. There was still goodness in her mother’s boy.

Meanwhile, Pen descended the stairs, heart heavy. He regretted how he’d treated Laura. Her gentle eyes haunted him. He almost turned back—but then remembered he’d left her with Pynsent. He couldn’t apologize in front of him. So instead, he decided to patch things up with the Frenchman.

Mirobolant was pacing the hall when Pen arrived. He looked cheerful, like a man who’d just discovered chocolate.

“I’ve got him in the coffee-room,” Strong said, grinning. “With pistols and a candle. Or swords on the beach? Mirobolant’s a dead shot with foils—he killed four guards in the July barricades.”

“Confound it,” Pen growled. “I can’t fight a cook!”

“He’s a Chevalier of July,” Strong reminded him. “They present arms to him in France.”

“And you expect me to go out with a servant?” Pen asked, outraged.

“You’ll invite me to a hair trigger?” Strong laughed. “No, I’m joking. I came to settle things. I’ve told Mirobolant you didn’t mean ‘cook’ as an insult. In England, it’s improper for a hired man to offer his arm to a daughter of the house.”

Then Strong dropped the bombshell: Mirobolant was secretly in love with Miss Blanche.

Pen burst out laughing. So did Strong. His anger vanished. He’d been jealous all evening—jealous of Pynsent, jealous of everything. He remembered his own past jealousy over Oaks. He could forgive anything in a man driven by such passion.

So he marched into the coffee-room, extended his hand, and delivered a speech in French so smooth it might have passed for Shakespeare translated.

“I am sincerely sorry for using a word that may have offended Monsieur Mirobolant,” he said. “And I give you my word as a gentleman that I never—*jamais*—intended…”

He paused, proud of his use of “intended,” though he hadn’t actually used the correct word. But Mirobolant was impressed.

“Bravo!” Strong cheered. “You speak French like Chateaubriand!”

“I’ve practiced since childhood,” Pen said.

Strong held back laughter for five minutes—then exploded. Pen never quite understood why.

By daybreak, they reached the Brawl. The ball was over. Madame Fribsby and Mirobolant were heading home. Laura was asleep, heart light. The Claverings were resting. And Blanche? She’d just returned from the refreshment-room, pale as a lemon ice, and told her maid about meeting the most romantic man ever—one who knew her father, the heroic, persecuted, murdered author of her being. She even began writing a sonnet to his ghost before bed.

Pen returned to Fairoaks, still without saying a word to Laura. But he didn’t mind. He’d wait until she came home. He wasn’t really jealous of Pynsent. After all, he was sure Laura would give in—just like her mother always did.

When Helen asked what happened, Pen laughed it off. “No declarations tonight,” he said. “Too much chaos. We’ll save sentiment for later.”

His mother kissed his forehead and patted his hair. She was convinced no woman could resist him.

That night, Pen packed books, cleaned his gun, and smoked pipes while talking to his dog, Ponto.

“I’ll take chambers,” he declared. “Enter the Inn of Court. With two hundred pounds, I’ll survive the first year. Then my pen will support me—tragedy, comedy, novel—all nearly done. I’ll live well without touching Mom’s money. Then, someday, I’ll return, marry Laura, and make her happy. She’s sweet, beautiful, and has given me her money. It’ll steady me, won’t it, Ponto?”

Ponto wagged his tail. Pen patted him. “Yes, she’ll steady me. Won’t you miss me, old boy?”

Ponto licked his hand. Pen smiled.

Laura returned late, driven by Pynsent. Pen’s brow darkened. Laura noticed and felt guilty. Helen, oblivious, rose to go to bed—only for Laura to say she was tired too. So the plan was delayed.

The next morning, Helen woke Pen early.

“Rouse up, dear Pen!” she said. “It’s a beautiful day. Laura’s in the garden.”

Pen laughed. He knew what she was up to.

“Ah, you artful creature,” he said, kissing her. “Can no one escape your tricks?”

Helen blushed, fluttered, and left him.

Pen shaved, dressed, and walked to the garden, feeling grim. “I’m about to tie myself to life,” he thought. “For my mother. Laura’s wonderful. She gave me her money. I wish I hadn’t taken it. But both women want this. So I’ll do it. A man may do worse than make two good people happy.”

In the garden, Laura wore her usual uniform: straw hat, ribbon streamer, blouse, belt, and gauntlets—looking like a soldier sent to rescue a damsel in distress.

She smiled—the same smile that had mocked him at the ball.

Pen scowled. “Why do you laugh at me?” he asked.

“My dear Arthur,” she said, “you looked so funny when you fell. I couldn’t help it.”

“Confound Miss Roundle,” he roared.

“She looked terrible,” Laura said, giggling. “But you were up in seconds. Poor Miss Roundle, sitting on the ground in her red crape dress—can I forget her?”

She mimicked Miss Roundle’s expression—then stopped. “Well, we shouldn’t laugh at her. But we should laugh at you, Pen, if you’re angry about a stumble.”

“You shouldn’t laugh at me, Laura,” Pen said, bitter.

“And why not? Are you such a great man?”

“No,” he said. “I’m such a poor one. Haven’t you baited me enough?”

“Dear Pen,” she said, holding out her hand. “If I’ve hurt you, I beg your pardon.”

“It’s your kindness that humiliates me more than your laughter,” Pen said. “You’re always my superior.”

“Superior to the great Arthur Pendennis?” she asked, teasing. “Impossible.”

“Those who confer benefits should not sneer,” he said. “I don’t like my benefactor laughing at me. It makes the debt harder to bear. You scorn me because I took your money. And I deserve it. But the blow hurts more coming from you.”

And so, beneath the morning sun, the future Mr. Pendennis stood in the garden, torn between duty, pride, and the fact that his fiancée was both adorable and terrifyingly honest.

Our dear reader, if you’ve been enjoying the tranquil woods, seaside breezes, and cozy gossip of Clavering, or the quiet misery of little Fairoaks, now it’s time to pack your bags and hop aboard the *Alacrity* coach with Arthur Pendennis—because London is calling, and he’s off to conquer it. No more country lanes, no more idle dreams: this is the big city, where fortunes are made, reputations are lost, and young men either become men or disappear into the fog like a poorly planned alibi.

As the coach rattles through the night, leaving behind the friendly gates of home (and the lingering scent of his mother’s lavender), Arthur is already busy planning his future. He’ll be wise. He’ll be prudent. He may even achieve fame—though he hasn’t quite decided whether he wants that yet. His first failure has taught him humility, but not defeat. It hasn’t broken his spirit, nor crushed his belief in himself—only made him slightly more aware that he might not be as brilliant as he once imagined. Still, he’s got ideas—hundreds of them—racing through his head like a herd of runaway squirrels. How much older he feels than when he last took this road, twelve months ago, on his way to Oxbridge! Then he was a boy. Now? He’s a man… or at least, he’s pretending to be one.

His thoughts drift tenderly toward his mother—blessed, trusting, loving her son despite all his past escapades. “Blessings be upon her!” he whispers to the stars. “Give me strength to work, to endure, to be honest, and avoid temptation.” He imagines she’s awake too, praying for him from the safety of Fairoaks, sending up purer petitions than any he could ever muster. Her love is his talisman, his anchor, his excuse for not completely falling apart. And then there’s Laura—whose affection he’d dearly loved to carry with him, but whom he knows he doesn’t deserve. He confesses it with shame: she’s better, nobler, cleaner than he’ll ever be. He feels like a wild thing drawn near a church—not out of hatred, but because he knows he doesn’t belong inside. He draws back from her purity like a drunkard from a hymn.

With such noble reflections, Pen finally dozes off just before dawn, only to wake refreshed at the old breakfast stop at B——, where he’s eaten many a merry meal since he was knee-high to a horse. As they leave, the sun bursts through, the coach picks up speed, the horn blares, and Pen, feeling gloriously alive, chats with the guard and fellow passengers. The world grows busier, livelier—until finally, the greys gallop out at H——, and the coach rolls into London.

Ah, London! What young man hasn’t felt a shiver run down his spine at the sight of it? Carriages clatter, crowds swarm, and the air hums with possibility. “Here it is,” thinks Pen. “My battlefield. My chance. I’ve been a dawdling boy—now I must prove I can be a man.” From his perch on the roof, he surveys the city like a soldier about to charge into battle—except instead of swords, he’s armed with ambition and a hundred pounds.

On the journey, he strikes up a conversation with a fellow traveler in a shabby cloak—Mr. Doolan, reporter for *The Star*, who claims to know every literary giant in England personally. Tom Campbell? Hood? Sydney Smith? He’s dined with them all. As they pass Brompton, Doolan points out Mr. Hurtle, the famed reviewer, strolling with an umbrella. Pen cranes over the coach, hoping for a glimpse. “A Boniface man,” says Pen. “Faith, he is,” replies Doolan, “and I’ve known him well.”

Pen feels honored to have seen the great Hurtle. He still believes in authors, editors, and literary greatness. Even Wagg, whose books seem rather pedestrian, is respected by Pen—especially when Doolan reveals he earns three hundred pounds per novel. Pen does some quick math: five thousand a year? Why, that’s possible! With the right book—and maybe a few more dinners with influential people—he might just make it.

At the Gloster Coffee-house, Pen meets his first London acquaintance: Harry Foker, prancing down Arlington Street in a cab pulled by a horse so large it looks like it might eat the carriage. Foker wears white kid gloves, white reins, and a respectable chin tuft. A tiny cab-boy, half his size, swings behind like a dangling ornament. Foker spots the dusty *Alacrity*, waves his whip, and shouts, “Hullo, Pen!”—a greeting so enthusiastic it makes Pen feel instantly at home.

Doolan, impressed by Pen’s connection to such a grand cab, decides he must dine with him. Pen, thrilled to be free and in London, invites him to the Covent Garden Coffee-house, where he’s booked a room. He calls a cab, rattles away, and arrives buzzing with energy. He greets the waiter, bows to the landlord, asks for the landlady, and mourns the absence of the old Boots. He’s got a hundred pounds in his pocket—enough to live like a king… for a week.

He dresses in his finest clothes, dines modestly on a pint of sherry (determined to be economical), and heads to the theatre next door.

The lights, music, crowd, and general gaiety enchant Pen. He laughs at jokes, applauds songs, and delights the weary regulars in the boxes, who’ve long since stopped caring about anything. After the first act, he struts through the lobby like a man who belongs in high society—though really, he’s just excited to be anywhere other than Fairoaks.

And there’s Foker again, this time with Grandy Tiptoff (of the Household Brigade) and Lord Colchicum, Captain Tiptoff’s uncle—a venerable peer who’s been chasing pleasure since the French Revolution began. They rush over to Pen, insisting he join their private box, where Miss Blenkinsop, the celebrated actress, sits with shoulders like moonlight and ringlets like rope. Behind her, snoozing in a wig, is her father—old Blenkinsop, the veteran actor known for playing “heavy fathers” both on stage and in life.

It’s eleven o’clock. At Fairoaks, Mrs. Pendennis lies awake, wondering if her son is safe. Laura, too, is awake—though what she’s thinking, we don’t know. Meanwhile, the farce continues, and Mrs. Leary, in a hussar jacket and braided pantaloons, enchants the audience with her charm and ballads.

Pen would love to watch her—but the box is full of chatter. Tiptoff knows where her maillots came from. Colchicum saw her debut in ’14. Miss Blenkinsop declares Mrs. Leary sings “out of all tune,” which shocks Pen, who thinks she sings like a nightingale and looks like an angel.

Then Hoppus appears as Sir Harcourt Featherby, and the men in the box complain he’s getting stale. Tiptoff suggests throwing Miss Blenkinsop’s bouquet to him.

“Not for the world!” she cries. “Lord Colchicum gave it to me.”

Pen remembers the name—and with a bow and a blush, he tells the peer he owes him thanks for proposing him at the Megatherium Club, at his uncle’s request.

“What, you’re Wigsby’s nephew?” says the peer. “We always call him Wigsby.” Pen blushes again. “We balloted you in last Wednesday. Your uncle wasn’t here.”

Delightful news! Pen thanks Lord Colchicum profusely, while the peer peers through his opera-glass like a hawk inspecting prey.

“Don’t be always looking at that box, you naughty creature,” says Miss Blenkinsop.

“She’s a dev’lish fine woman, that Mirabel,” says Tiptoff. “Though Mirabel was a d——d fool to marry her.”

“A stupid old spooney,” says the peer.

“Mirabel!” cries Pendennis.

“Ha! ha!” laughs Foker. “We’ve heard of her before, haven’t we, Pen?”

It’s his first love—Miss Fotheringay, now Lady Mirabel, wife of Sir Charles Mirabel, G.C.B., former envoy to Pumpernickel. She once captured his heart. Now, seeing her again—through the lens of experience—Pen wonders how he ever thought she was worth loving.

So, on his very first night in London, Arthur Pendennis is introduced to a club, an actress, a heavy father of the stage, and a glittering circle of jovial blades—old and young alike. Lord Colchicum, though ancient and bald, still drinks claret like a champion and hosts dinners at Richmond and Greenwich. He loves the theatre, welcomes actors from every nation, and entertains young men of fashion at his Thames-side villa—especially the ladies of the greenroom, whom he finds more polished than their male counterparts.

The next day, Pen pays his entrance fee at the Megatherium Club—exactly one-third of his hundred pounds—and enjoys lunch with immense satisfaction. He sinks into an easy chair, tries to read magazines, and wonders if the members are staring at him. He writes a letter on the club’s paper, declaring how comforting it will be after a hard day’s work.

Then he visits his uncle’s lodgings in Bury Street, trembling slightly, as requested by his mother. But the Major isn’t home. The apartment is empty—brown holland covers the table, bills and letters wait on the mantelpiece. The Major is abroad, in Badnbadn, with the Marcus of Steyne. Pen leaves his card—still bearing the address “Fairoaks.”

When the Major returns in November, he finds another card—this one reading “Lamb Court, Temple”—and a note from Arthur and his mother saying he’s come to town, joined the Upper Temple, and is studying hard for the bar.

Lamb Court? The Major recalls hearing of it—some fashionable ladies dined with Mr. Ayliffe, a barrister “in society.” Mr. Deuceace, Lord Crabs’s son, had lived there too. He sends Morgan to investigate.

“What sort of place is it, Morgan?” the Major asks, peering out of bed-curtains in the thick yellow fog.

“I should say rayther a shy place, sir,” Morgan replies. “The lawyers live there, names on the doors. Mr. Harthur lives three pair high. Mr. Warrington too.”

“Suffolk Warringtons? Good family,” thinks the Major. “Comfortable rooms, eh?”

“Honly saw the outside, sir. Nasty black staircase. Wonder how a gentleman can live in such a place.”

“Who taught you where gentlemen should live, Morgan?” the Major retorts. “Arthur’s studying law. That’s good enough for me.”

“Boys will be boys,” the Major muses later. “He wrote a decent letter. Colchicum says he’s a gentlemanlike lad. His mother’s a saint. If he sows his wild oats and sticks to his work, he might do well. Think of Charley Mirabel marrying that Fotheringay! Foolish old man. He doesn’t want to come here until I give permission—that’s manly. I was angry with him before. Gad, I’ll go see him.”

And so, the Major takes a city omnibus to the Upper Temple.

Arriving at the dingy gate, he’s led through dark alleys and melancholy archways until he reaches Lamb Court. If Pall Mall is dim, Lamb Court is pitch-black. Candles burn in rooms—pupils scribble declarations under tallow lamps, clerks chat patronizingly, and wigmaker Curling displays judges’ wigs like ghostly serpents. Two clerks toss halfpennies under a lamp. A laundress in pattens enters. A newspaper boy exits. A porter paces slowly. The whole place reeks of damp, despair, and legal ambition.

The Major shudders. “Good Ged! The poor boy mustn’t live on here.”

He climbs the abominable black stairs, groping along damp banisters, until he reaches the third floor. A candle burns in the passage. Doors open. Names: Mr. Warrington, Mr. A. Pendennis.

An Irish charwoman opens the door.

“Is that the beer?” booms a voice.

A man sits on a table, unshaven, smoking a pipe. Pen sits in a chair, legs near the fire, cigar in hand. A little boy grins at the Major, mistaking him for beer.

“Pen, my boy—it’s I,” says the Major, choking on smoke. “Your uncle.”

Warrington rises, Pen jumps up. “Beg your pardon,” says Warrington, loud and frank. “Will you take a cigar? Clear those things off the chair, Pidgeon.”

Pen throws his cigar into the grate. The Major shakes his hand warmly.

After greetings, Pen introduces Warrington—an old Boniface man, younger son of Sir Miles Warrington of Suffolk. The Major nods approval. He served with Warrington’s uncle in India and New South Wales.

“Took a sheep-farm there,” says Warrington. “Made a fortune—better than law or soldiering. Think I shall go there too.”

Beer arrives in a tankard with a glass bottom. Warrington laughs, takes a long draught, wipes his wrist across his beard. He’s dressed in a ragged shooting jacket, blue-bearded, drinking like a coalheaver—but unmistakably a gentleman.

He leaves, letting the Major and Pen talk.

“Rough and ready, your chum seems,” says the Major.

“Times are altered,” says Pen, blushing. “Warrington’s just called, no business, but he knows law. Until I can afford a pleader, I use his books.”

“Is that one of the books?” the Major asks, smiling.

A French novel lies at Pen’s feet.

“This is not a working day, sir,” says Pen. “We were out late last night—at Lady Whiston’s. Everybody in town was there except you—Counts, Ambassadors, Turks, Stars and Garters. My name’s in the paper too.”

He laughs. “I met an old flame—Lady Mirabel. She shook hands, was gracious. I may thank you for being out of that scrape. She presented me to the husband—an old beau in a star and blonde wig. He doesn’t seem very wise. She asked me to call. I may go now without fear of losing my heart.”

“What, new loves?” asks the Major, chuckling.

“Two or three,” says Pen. “But I don’t put on my grand serieux anymore. That goes off after the first flame.”

And so, Arthur Pendennis begins his life in London—not with grandeur, but with smoke, beer, and the quiet hope that someday, he might actually become someone worth remembering.

Let’s talk about the Inns of Court—those ancient, creaking fortresses of legal learning where time seems to have taken a holiday and left behind only dust, damp, and the faint echo of forgotten arguments. They’re like museums of old customs, where men still wear wigs in court (though not always on their heads), and where the very air smells of parchment, pipe smoke, and mild regret.

Now, don’t get me wrong: I’m not saying these places are modern marvels. On the contrary, they’re architectural relics—buildings that were erected before indoor plumbing was invented, before anyone thought cleanliness might be next to godliness, and certainly before anyone dreamed of shower-baths. Imagine this: you're a barrister, earning your living by interpreting statutes written in Latin by people who probably never took a bath themselves. And yet, you live in a room so small it could fit inside a wardrobe, paying rent that would buy you a decent cottage with a garden and a goat.

Meanwhile, down in Spitalfields, a poor mechanic has running water, a cistern, and a sink—things that seem almost scandalous when compared to the noble lawyer’s daily ritual of fetching water from a jug carried up by a laundress who also happens to be responsible for mopping the stairs and occasionally spilling her bucket over the head of Mr. Grump, a gentleman of more than half a century who lives directly beneath Warrington and Pendennis.

Mr. Grump is a man of principle—though perhaps not much else. He believes shower-baths are “an absurd, newfangled, dandified folly,” especially since one of them, installed by Warrington and Pendennis, leaks through the ceiling into his chambers like a disgruntled ghost. Every morning he wakes to the sound of water gushing above him, as if some divine punishment were being administered via hot tap. He curses the laundress, the builders, the entire notion of hygiene, and once said publicly that if he’d known how wet things would get, he’d have stayed in the country and become a shepherd.

But let’s not dwell too long on Mr. Grump. Let’s look around the Temple itself—the Upper Temple, to be precise—where history hangs thick in the air like bad breath after a feast. The walls are adorned with coats of arms belonging to knights, baronets, lords, and gentlemen whose names now mean nothing to anyone except those who study heraldry or enjoy confusing genealogies. You’d think one of them, at least, might have had the decency to install a proper water system for future generations. But no. Instead, we have Pump Court and Fountain Court—names that suggest refreshment and joy—but where no bencher has ever been seen diving into the fountain, nor any junior counsel cooling off during a particularly dull case.

Still, there’s something oddly charming about the place. It’s like an old boarding house run by ghosts. The students come and go, the benchers sit at high table, and everyone eats soup, meat, tarts, and port wine under portraits of judges who probably never ate anything but cold beef and black coffee.

And here’s where our hero, Arthur Pendennis, enters the scene—not as a scholar, nor a saint, but as a young man trying to find his way in life without quite knowing what direction he’s heading. He’s not exactly studying law with religious devotion—he’s reading, yes, but mostly because it keeps him out of trouble. His uncle thinks he’s turning over a new leaf, writing home proudly that “the lad has sown his wild oats and is becoming quite steady.” Which is true—except that the oats he’s sowing are mostly in taverns, and the steadiness comes from having a good friend named Warrington.

Warrington is the kind of fellow who makes you feel warm just looking at him. He wears clothes that look like they’ve survived several wars, but he’s got a smile that could melt ice. He’s a baronet’s son, which gives him a certain social standing, but he doesn’t care about it. He prefers smoking pipes, telling stories, and laughing at the absurdities of human nature. When he first meets Pendennis in Hall, they recognize each other instantly—not because they’ve met before, but because they both went to Boniface College and thrashed the same bargeman named Bill Simes.

“Stunning Warrington,” says Pendennis, delighted.
“Yes,” replies Warrington, “I recollect you in your freshman’s term. But you appear to have cut me out.”

They become fast friends immediately. And soon enough, Pendennis moves into Lamb Court, sharing rooms with Warrington, leaving behind the sterile elegance of St. James’s and embracing the rougher pleasures of bachelor life: cheap dinners, late-night talks, and occasional visits to the opera—though usually only if someone else pays.

Now, about dinner at the Temple Hall: it’s a spectacle. Students sit in messes of four, each with a joint of meat, a pie, and a bottle of port. But clever fellows like Lowton know tricks. For instance, wait until the benchers finish their meal—there are only three of them, but ten dishes—and then sneak over to see what’s left. “Wait a bit,” says Lowton, pulling Pendennis’s sleeve. “The side-tables are full, and there’s only three benchers to eat ten dishes—if we wait, perhaps we shall get something from their table.”

And sure enough, sometimes they do. The benchers often send down their fish—trout, salmon, even turtle—to the senior table. Lowton is a master strategist. He wants to be captain of the mess so he can claim the thirteenth glass of port—a tradition so sacred it borders on religion.

“Ah, Mr. Lowton,” says Pendennis, “you’re a sly fellow.”
“Well,” replies Lowton modestly, “I’ve lived in London most of my life. Of course I’ve got my eyes about me.”

He points out the notable guests: Boosey the bencher, Balls the King’s Counsel, Swettenham, and Old Grump, who’s been dining there for forty years. Then there are the swells—Trail, Ringwood, Bob Suckling—who don’t actually eat anything. They’re merely pretending to dine, so they can later boast they were at the Temple Hall while really feasting at a club or a grand dinner party. Their names appear in the *Morning Post*, but their stomachs remain empty. That’s why Lowton likes dining with them—they don’t drink, so the port goes to whoever’s willing.

“You’re green,” says Lowton, shaking his head. “You don’t understand the ways of the world.”

Pendennis learns quickly. Especially when Warrington arrives, dressed in ragged clothes but radiating charm. He sits down at the table, smiles at Pendennis, and says, “We ought to know each other. We’re both Boniface men. My name’s Warrington.”

And thus begins a friendship that will carry them through many adventures, legal mishaps, and questionable choices.

After dinner, Warrington invites Pendennis back to his rooms for a pipe. “Come on,” he says. “It’s short. We’ll talk about old times.”

And so they do. They talk about college, about girls, about dreams, about the fact that neither of them is likely to make it to the Bench or the Woolsack anytime soon. But they don’t mind. Life is better when shared with a friend who laughs at the same jokes and understands the value of a quiet evening with tobacco and conversation.

Later, Pendennis gives up his lodgings in St. James’s and moves in with Warrington. Pidgeon, the servant boy, and Flanagan, the laundress, now split their duties between the two friends. It’s simpler, cheaper, and infinitely more fun.

So there you have it—the Temple, the lawyers, the customs, the quirks, and the friendships formed in dimly lit chambers and noisy halls. A place where history lingers, where progress is slow, and where the greatest achievement may be simply surviving dinner without being caught stealing a piece of trout from the benchers’ table.

And if you ever pass by the Temple Garden, keep your eyes open—you might just catch sight of Sir Roger de Coverley strolling with Mr. Spectator, discussing the latest fashion in hoops and patches, or Harry Fielding scribbling away at midnight, ink on his ruffles, towel on his head, chasing fame one article at a time.

But for now, let’s leave them to their dreams—and return to our own, which, like Pendennis’s, may not be perfect, but are certainly more enjoyable when shared with a good friend and a pipe.

CHAPTER XXXI. Old and New Acquaintances (Rewritten for Humor)

Pen, newly armed with the noble ambition to *see life*, plunged headfirst into London’s most peculiar haunts like a young explorer discovering the Amazon—only instead of jaguars and piranhas, he found coalheavers, boxers, and men who could drink beer faster than you could say “tavern.” He fancied himself a social anthropologist, sampling the lower orders as though they were exotic fruits from some far-flung island of human eccentricity. He’d have gladly shared a pint with a gang of burglars if only one had invited him over for tea. Alas, no such luck.

But Warrington, his trusty companion in crime and culture, was already well acquainted with the city’s underbelly. He knew every pub within a ten-mile radius and could name the landlord of each like a man who’d once been a minor nobleman in a bad novel. He wasn’t just welcome at the bar—he was practically family. And honestly? He liked these folks better than his own class. “Society,” he declared, “is like a ballroom full of identical twins wearing the same hats and saying ‘How do you do?’ in unison. But here?” He gestured grandly at a coalheaver mid-sneeze. “Here, there’s *character*. I’d rather talk to the strongest man in England than to another fop who thinks a waistcoat is a statement of genius.”

Pen followed Warrington around like a royal prince visiting the peasants—except this prince had no crown, no kingdom, and only two pennies halfpenny in his pocket. Still, he carried himself with all the dignity of a man who believed he was born to rule, even if his domain was limited to a single room above a pawnbroker’s shop. The working folk adored him. They called him “a high chap,” “a regular swell,” and once, after he tipped the waiter five shillings for bringing a plate of cold mutton, they named a sandwich after him: the “Pendennis Special” (though it contained no actual Pendennis).

It was a glorious time to be young and foolish. Pen spent mornings reading law books, yes—but also politics, history, literature, and occasionally a pamphlet on the moral implications of eating cheese before bedtime. Then, when the clock struck noon, he’d burst out into the streets with Warrington, appetite roaring like a lion that hadn’t eaten since Tuesday, ready to feast on laughter, ale, and the sheer joy of being alive. At twenty-four, everything felt fresh—like the world had just woken up and said, “Hey, look at me! I’m new!”

And then came the inevitable descent into middle age. You know how it goes: the lights go out, the garlands fray, the pleasure garden turns into a muddy patch where someone left a broken umbrella. Grey hairs appear like morning fog—and worse, a headache follows close behind. Pleasure, once radiant and rosy-cheeked, retires to bed early, still wearing her rouge. Ah, friend. Let us walk through the day sober and sad, but friendly. That’s the way.

Now, imagine this scene: dawn breaking over London, bridges glowing pink, the city waking up like a cat stretching after a long nap. And what do we see? Mr. Pen and Mr. Warrington, rattling down the cobbles toward the Temple, reeking of brandy and regret. They’ve just survived one of their legendary nights—wild, yes, but not *that* wild. Warrington, bless him, hated women; Pen, while capable of falling head over heels for a fool (and likely did so more than once), always treated them like goddesses—until they turned out to be mere mortals. Then he’d politely retreat, muttering about “unfortunate circumstances.”

And speaking of unfortunate circumstances—there was an old friend waiting for Pen at the Fielding’s Head, a tavern so ancient it probably predates the invention of the word “pub.” This was no ordinary drinking hole. It was a shrine to song, camaraderie, and questionable hygiene. Its most famous feature? The Back Kitchen, where a little club met nightly to sing songs so heartfelt they made grown men cry—especially when Cutts, the landlord, belted out “The Good Old English Gentleman” or “Dear Tom, this Brown Jug.” His voice was like a barrel of rum with a slight cough.

And then there was Captain Costigan—yes, *the* Captain Costigan, father of the woman who once held Pen’s heart like a sacred relic. Now he was a fixture at the Fielding’s Head, known for three things: 1) telling everyone about his daughter’s marriage, 2) claiming he’d draw £100 from his son-in-law next Saturday (which never happened), and 3) borrowing money from anyone within earshot until payday rolled around—then showing up with banknotes like a phoenix rising from the ashes of debt.

Pen found him one evening, swaggering near the singers’ table, ordering brandy-and-water like it was the nectar of the gods. Warrington, having made friends with the bass singer, joined him, and Pen followed—only to freeze when he saw the Captain. He’d just come from Lady Whiston’s party, where he’d seen Costigan’s daughter again. A wave of nostalgia hit him. He stepped forward, hand outstretched, eyes bright.

“Captain!” he said warmly.

The Captain looked at him, blinked, then squinted like a man trying to read a menu in dim light. “Grecious Heavens!” he bellowed. “Is it possible? Me dear boy, me dear fellow, me dear friend…” Then he paused. “I know your face… but, bedad, I’ve forgot your name.”

Five years of punch had done their work. Pen laughed, though inwardly he winced. “Don’t you remember me, Captain? I’m Pendennis—Arthur Pendennis, of Chatteris.”

The sound of his name snapped the Captain back to reality. “Ah! Me gallant young friend! My noble collagian!” he cried, slapping Pen’s hand like it was a drum. “How’s your mother? Your guardian? The General? The Major? I preshoom, from your apparance, you’ve come into your prawpertee. And, bedad, yee’ll spend it like a man of spirit—I’ll go bail for that.”

Pen smiled. “Not yet, Captain. But thank you for the offer.”

“And if ye want any thrifle,” the Captain boomed, “there’s poor old Jack Costigan has got a guinea or two in his pocket—and, by heavens! you shall never want, Awthur, me dear boy. What’ll ye have? John, come hither, and look aloive! Give this gentleman a glass of punch, and I’ll pay for’t.”

Then, turning to Warrington, he added, “Your friend? I’ve seen him before. Permit me to have the honour of making meself known to ye, sir, and requesting ye’ll take a glass of punch.”

Pen thought, *I don’t envy Sir Charles Mirabel his father-in-law.*

He asked about Bows, the pianist from their youth. “Have you any news of him, Captain?”

“No doubt he’s very well,” the Captain replied, jingling coins and whistling “The Little Doodeen”—a tune he sang so often it was rumored to be his last words. “Me dear boy—I’ve forgot your name again—but my name’s Costigan, Jack Costigan, and I’d loike ye to take as many tumblers of punch in my name as ever ye loike. Ye know my name; I’m not ashamed of it.”

And so he rambled on.

At that moment, the pianist began to play. Pen glanced over—and there was Bows, sitting at the battered piano like a ghost from the past. The instrument looked like it had survived a war and several bad decisions. Bows played with quiet grace, accompanying the singers with a touch that spoke of memory and melancholy.

Pen recognized the tune instantly: the chorus from *The Stranger*, the one that played just before Mrs. Haller entered. His heart skipped a beat. Nobody else noticed. They were too busy shouting for poached eggs and kidneys.

When the piece ended, Pen walked over and shook Bows’s hand. “What, you haven’t forgot the old tune, Mr. Pendennis?” Bows said, grinning. “I thought you’d remember it. I take it, it was the first tune of that sort you ever heard played—wasn’t it, sir? You were quite a young chap then.”

“I fear the Captain’s very bad tonight,” Bows added. “He breaks out on pay-day. And I shall have the deuce’s own trouble getting home. We live together. We still hang on, sir, in partnership, though Miss Em—though my lady Mirabel has left the firm.”

He took a sip of porter. “And so you remember old times, do you? Wasn’t she a beauty, sir?”

Pen nodded. “She was.”

They would meet again, of course. And Pen would continue to visit the Fielding’s Head, where all sorts of people passed through: country farmers, apprentices, medical students with dirty coats and loud opinions, university bucks with smirks that could curdle milk, guardsmen with shiny buttons, and even lords and senators—because apparently, no one was too important to enjoy a decent song and a bad meal.

One night, the bass singer, Hodgen, performed his smash-hit “The Body Snatcher.” He appeared on stage in full regalia: coffin, spade, skull-candlestick, flask of gin. The song was terrifying—so much so that the glasses trembled on the tables. Poor Cos’s “The Little Doodeen” was sung afterward, but only by those brave enough to stay after the resurrectionist chant.

Then came two familiar faces: Mr. Hoolan and Mr. Doolan, editors of rival newspapers—the *Dawn* (Liberal) and the *Day* (ultra-Conservative). They sat down, papers stained with porter, and began discussing foreign mails, correspondents, and circulation figures like two generals planning a battle.

Warrington picked up the *Dawn* and pointed to an article attacking the *Day* for employing “professional bullies” to smear reputations. “As rogues of note in former days employed assassins,” it read, “our contemporary, the *Day*, employs smashers out of doors to utter forgeries.”

Hoolan chuckled. “Kidneys, John, and a glass of stout. How are you, Morgan?”

“Doing pretty well, thank ye, Mick,” said Doolan. “Faith she’s accustomed to it.”

“Don’t bring Patsey with you, Mick,” Morgan warned. “Our Georgy’s got the measles.”

They talked shop—foreign correspondents, courier costs, circulation wars—while Warrington whispered to Pen, “They’re talking about Doctor Boyne and Captain Shandon—Irish controversialists who fight in print but are best friends in real life. They abuse England more in one article than we could in a dozen volumes.”

Doolan, meanwhile, turned to Pen. “Mr. Pendennis, I am delighted to have the honour of seeing ye again. The night’s journey on the top of the Alacrity was one of the most agreeable I ever enjoyed in my life, and it was your liveliness and urbanity that made the trip so charming.”

Pen blinked. He didn’t remember being particularly lively.

“I have often thought over that happy night,” Doolan continued, “and talked over it to Mrs. Doolan. I have seen your elegant young friend, Mr. Foker, too. He is an occasional frequenter of this hostelry, and a right good one it is.”

Then he bowed slightly. “Mr. Pendennis, when I saw you I was on the *Tom and Jerry Weekly Paper*; I now have the honour to be sub-editor of the *Dawn*, one of the best-written papers of the empire.”

Pen nodded. He wondered if the *Dawn* had a section on etiquette for polite conversations with gentlemen who used “honour” like a seasoning.

And so the night wore on—laughter, songs, secrets, and the faint smell of regret. For Pen, it was a reunion of old friends, old tunes, and old dreams. And somewhere, in the corner, Captain Costigan was already planning to borrow another half-crown.

Arthur Pendennis, in the midst of his modest but spirited revels—feasting on porter and beef, laughing with friends, and living like a young prince who’d never heard of a budget—began to notice a certain shadow looming over his head. Not a ghost, nor a dragon, but something far more terrifying: the last five-pound note in his pocketbook. It lay there like a sleeping lion, quiet for now, but ready to pounce when its time came.

His money had dwindled faster than a candle in a drafty room. A third had vanished into club dues. Another chunk had gone toward a wardrobe of furniture that included a bed, a chair, and a mirror that reflected not just his face, but his growing anxiety. And now? Nothing left. No inheritance to tap (he wouldn’t dream of it), no uncle to bail him out (his uncle was busy dining with dignitaries and pretending he wasn’t related to anyone who ever paid rent), and no mother to scold him into responsibility—though she lived quietly in the country, content to send her son letters full of love and vague advice.

Warrington, his comrade and confidant (though not yet fully aware of Pen’s financial plight), assumed Arthur was a man of means. After all, he wore gold chains, carried a dressing case that could have belonged to a duke, and dressed as if every morning were a royal levee. He didn’t skimp on cabs—even in fine weather—and always tipped the driver *too* generously, as though tipping were a form of spiritual offering. He couldn’t bring himself to buy cheap gloves or use a handkerchief twice. If a beggar stood at the door with eyes like broken marbles, Pen would drop a sovereign without hesitation. “It’s not charity,” he’d say. “It’s justice.”

But this generosity, noble as it was, wasn’t helping him eat.

One evening, while inspecting the change from his final five-pound note—lying on a tray beside Warrington’s ale—Pen sighed dramatically.

“It is the last rose of summer,” he declared, quoting poetry as if it were prophecy. “Its blooming companions have long since faded. Behold—the last one has shed its leaves.” Then, with a sigh that could have toppled a small church spire, he told Warrington the whole story: his mother’s modest income, his own college extravagances, Laura’s kindness, and the fact that he was officially, definitively, *out* of cash.

Warrington listened, puffing thoughtfully on his pipe. When Pen finished, he knocked the ashes out with a flourish.

“Impecuniosity,” said Warrington, “is the best tonic for an honest man. It wakes you up. It forces you to look your fate in the eye. Like a man riding a fence—you brace yourself. You discover what you can live without. No new boots. No eau de Cologne. No cab unless you’re being chased by a griffin. You’ve been spoiled, Pen. Spoiled by women. Spoiled by privilege. A man with health and brains who can’t earn a living doesn’t deserve to be here. Let him jump off Waterloo Bridge. Or steal a leg of mutton and go to Australia. I don’t care which. But let him *do* something.”

“You’re cruel,” said Pen. “But how do I earn anything?”

Warrington laughed. “Why not advertise in *The Times*? ‘Gentleman, B.A., formerly plucked at St. Boniface College, seeks position as usher in classical and commercial academy. Will teach Latin, French, mathematics, and piano accompaniment. Also skilled in hair-cutting and general pupil supervision.’ Address: A. P., Lamb Court, Temple.”

“Confound you!” cried Pen.

“Or consider Bloundell,” Warrington continued. “He’s a professional blackleg—travels Europe, seduces rich boys, then fleeces them like sheep. Bob O’Toole drives a mail coach. There’s a doctor’s son I know—quarreled with his father over finances, grew a mustache, moved to a provincial town, and announced himself as Professor Spineto, chiropodist to the Emperor of All the Russians. He operated on the editor of the local paper, became famous, and lived happily for three years. Now he’s back with his family and inherits the gallypots.”

“Hang gallypots!” said Pen. “I can’t cut corns, cheat at cards, or drive a stagecoach.”

“Then there’s journalism,” Warrington said, leaning forward. “Every man has secrets. Before you told me about your finances, I thought you were born with a silver spoon and a title. But now I see—your mother can’t support you forever. You must pay off Laura. You must stop being a burden. So what’s left? Work. Or die.”

“But how?” asked Pen.

“How do *I* live?” Warrington replied. “On my younger brother’s allowance—until I spent it five years ago. Then I did what any sensible man does: I went to work. I write.”

He pulled out a few sheets of paper. “These are mine. Articles in the *Law Reviews*. Occasionally, I contribute to a newspaper—*The Dawn*, edited by a friend.” He took Pen to the club one day and pointed silently to two articles. Pen read them, then stared. The style was unmistakable—sharp, witty, learned. He recognized it instantly.

“I can’t do that,” said Pen. “I don’t understand politics or history. I only know a little about books.”

“But you have something better,” Warrington said kindly. “You have *poetry*. I’ve seen your verses. They’re not bad. In fact, they’re good. You’ve got the spark. The real fire. Not oil-lamps, but torchlight.”

Arthur blinked. Tears formed. “You think so?”

“I know so,” said Warrington. “And I like you. I was lonely in chambers. You walked in, laughed at Lowton—poor little snob—and I liked that. I wanted someone. And you’re it.”

Pen felt a warm glow. “Thank you,” he said. “I’ve often thought I might be a poet.”

“Ah, yes,” said Warrington. “But let’s be honest. That poem you wrote at eighteen—*Ariadne in Naxos*? It’s the most sentimental, ridiculous rubbish I’ve ever read. And the Prize Poem? Pompous. Feeble. I’m amazed it didn’t win. You’re not Milton. You’re not Aeschylus. You’re not even Pindar. But you can write a decent article. And a pretty poem. That’s enough.”

Pen stamped his foot. “By Jove! I’ll show you!”

Warrington just laughed and blew twenty-four puffs of smoke into the air—like a tiny volcano of amusement.

And soon enough, opportunity knocked.

Mr. Bacon, the eminent publisher of Paternoster Row (formerly of “Bacon and Bungay”), was preparing his annual literary miscellany, the *Spring Annual*. It was edited by Lady Violet Lebas and featured poems by young noblemen—Lord Dodo, Percy Popjoy, Bedwin Sands—all eager to prove they weren’t entirely useless. The book also included illustrations—lovely, romantic scenes—except that sometimes the poets failed to rise to the occasion.

One such failure happened to be Percy Popjoy’s contribution to a plate titled *The Church Porch*. It showed a Spanish maiden rushing to church with a prayer book, while a young man in a cloak watched from a niche. The picture was charming. The poem? Horrific.

Warrington read it and burst out laughing. Mr. Hack, Bacon’s reader, groaned. “We can’t publish this,” he said. “The public will riot. Bungay’s people are putting out a strong rival. We need something better. Lady Violet is busy with her own poem. What do we do?”

Warrington grinned. “I know a fellow who can write. Let me take the plate home. Send for the verses tomorrow. Pay well, of course.”

“Of course,” said Hack.

So Warrington returned to Pen’s chambers with the plate.

“Here’s your chance,” he said. “Write some verses to this. Earn your keep.”

“What?” said Pen. “A lady entering a church. A youth ogling her from a wine-shop window. What am I supposed to do?”

“Try,” said Warrington. “You’ve been waiting for this moment.”

Pen sat down, frowned, and after several hours of wrestling with inspiration (and possibly a glass of brandy), produced this:

---

Although I enter not,
Yet round about the spot
Sometimes I hover,
And at the sacred gate,
With longing eyes I wait,
Expectant of her.

The Minster bell tolls out
Above the city’s rout
And noise and humming.
They’ve stopped the chiming bell,
I hear the organ’s swell—
She’s coming, she’s coming!

My lady comes at last,
Timid and stepping fast,
And hastening hither,
With modest eyes downcast.
She comes—she’s here—she’s past.
May Heaven go with her!

Kneel undisturb’d, fair saint,
Pour out your praise or plaint
Meekly and duly.
I will not enter there,
To sully your pure prayer
With thoughts unruly.

But suffer me to pace
Round the forbidden place,
Lingering a minute,
Like outcast spirits, who wait
And see through Heaven’s gate
Angels within it.

---

When Warrington read it, he nodded slowly. “That’ll do.”

“Really?” said Pen.

“Better than Popjoy’s nonsense,” said Warrington. “Now, get another piece. We’ll ask for two guineas a page. If they like it, you’ll get into the magazines. Maybe even become famous.”

Pen found another ballad and handed both over. Together, they marched to Paternoster Row.

Bacon’s shop was old and low-browed, with a bust of Lord Verulam above the door. Opposite, Bungay’s establishment gleamed with fresh paint and seventeenth-century charm—like a museum of manners. Pen waited outside, pacing nervously, watching the world go by: penny dreadfuls, religious tracts, rosaries, sermons against Rome, and pamphlets titled *Come Out of Rome!*—all under the watchful eye of Saint Paul’s bells.

Inside, Warrington played his game. He knew Bacon loved being treated with casual disrespect by gentlemen. So he put on his hat and addressed the publisher from across the table.

“What! Not know Mr. Pendennis?” he said. “You can’t live much in the world, or you’d know him. Man of property in the West. Ancient family. Cousin to Lord Pontypool. Dines at Gaunt House every week.”

“Law bless me!” said Bacon. “Really?”

“I’ve just shown Mr. Hack some verses he wrote last night. Hack says he’ll give him a copy of the book—the what-d’-you-call-’em.”

“The *Spring Annual*,” said Bacon.

“Yes. And you don’t suppose a man like Pendennis gives up dinner at Gaunt House for nothing, do you?”

“No, sir,” said Bacon. “Fashionable men want to be paid.”

“I tell you—he’s a star. He’ll make a name.”

“They’ve said that before,” sighed Bacon. “Lord Viscount Dodo—sold eighty copies. Popjoy’s *Hadgincourt*—died on the shelf.”

“Well,” said Warrington, rising, “then I’ll take my man to Bungay.”

At that, Bacon surrendered. “Law bless you, give him a check.”

Warrington returned, grinning, and handed Pen the paper.

Pen nearly fainted. “I’ve earned it!” he shouted. “I can pay Laura! I can help my mother! I can live on my own!”

“I can marry the grand vizier’s daughter!” Warrington joked. “Buy a house in Belgrave Square! Build a castle in the air!”

They celebrated with a magnum of claret at the club. Pen paid. It was the first time in months he’d done so without guilt.

And just when he thought things couldn’t get better…

A printer’s boy appeared at the door.

“From Captain Shandon, sir,” he said, handing over a letter. Then he sat down on the bench and fell asleep.

Pen opened it.

> **F. P. Tuesday Morning.**
> My dear Sir,—
> Bungay will be here today about the *Pall Mall Gazette*. You’d be the very man to write a genuine West-end article—dashing, trenchant, and d—— aristocratic. Lady Hipshaw will contribute, but she’s not much. We’ve two lords—but the less they do, the better. We *must* have you. Your terms are yours. We’ll make a hit.
> Shall B. come to see you, or can you visit us?
> Ever yours,

Pen looked at Warrington. “The printer’s devil has come to the door,” he said.

“And brought the key,” Warrington replied.

Our imprisoned Captain, with all the fire and flourish of a man who’d just been released from a long sentence of silence, launched into his prospectus like a politician at a rally—only this one was held in a prison cell, with a publisher whose idea of literary critique was “That’s a dig at Bacon’s people!” He declared, with the solemnity of a prophet announcing Armageddon, that the time had come for the gentlemen of England to rise up and defend their ancient rights, their glorious order, and their sacred traditions—all of which were under siege by foreign revolutions, domestic radicals, cotton lords with bad manners, and the masses, who, despite being led by mill-owners, somehow managed to be both stupid and dangerous.

“The monarchy,” thundered Captain Shandon, “is insulted by a rabble of republican swine! The Church is abandoned by dissenters and undermined by infidels who sneak about like moles in a cheese shop! Our noble institutions—those that made England great and the title of ‘English Gentleman’ the proudest in Christendom—are left undefended, exposed to mockery by men who believe nothing holy, know nothing of history, and obey no law except when it suits them.”

He paused for effect. Bungay nodded wisely, as if he’d just heard the final word on constitutional law. Warrington burst out laughing.

“See how our dear friend Bungay reacts,” said Shandon, peering up from his papers with the air of a man conducting a psychological experiment. “That’s the true test. I’ve used Wellington and Waterloo a hundred times—and never once has the Duke failed to stir the soul.”

Bungay clapped his walking stick on the floor. “Hang him, pitch into him, Capting!” he roared, delighted. Then he turned to Warrington, wagging his head like a dog who’d just been told he could eat the whole pie. “For a slashing article, sir, there’s nobody like the Capting—no-obody!”

And so the Captain continued, with the candour of a man who’d just confessed to stealing a neighbour’s goose but still wanted to be admired. He admitted that until now, the gentlemen of England had been too busy managing their estates, settling their legal affairs, or simply ignoring politics altogether, to take charge of their own interests. Instead, they’d left their political representation to professional hacks—just as they left their wills to solicitors and their dinners to cooks. But no more! The time had come for gentlemen to champion themselves. Their enemies were bold, numerous, and utterly unyielding. They must fight back—not with hired wordsmiths, but with their own pens and swords.

“And let me tell you,” Shandon added, “Grub Street printing Gazettes from Whitehall—that’s a dig at Bacon’s people, Mr. Bungay.”
Bungay clapped again. “Pitch into him, Capting!” he cried.

The Captain then revealed that certain gentlemen—whose names, for obvious reasons, couldn’t be mentioned (which caused Warrington to snort with laughter)—had decided to launch a new journal. Its principles? Well, they were noble, patriotic, and entirely self-serving. These men, said Shandon, flourished his paper like a banner, were proud of their birth, loyal to their king, devoted to their church, and deeply committed to preserving England as the greatest and freest nation in the world—“and yes, the gentlemen of England (we’ll have that in large caps, Bungay, my boy) have made it so!”

Then came the dramatic climax: “And since some of these gentlemen are descended from those who signed the Magna Carta at Runnymede—”

“What’s that?” asked Bungay.

“An ancestor of mine sealed it with his sword-hilt,” Pen said gravely.

“It’s the Habeas Corpus, Mr. Bungay,” Warrington corrected.

“Ah, right, I dare say,” Bungay yawned, though he added, “Go on, Capting.”

So the Captain did. He explained that these unnamed gentlemen—likely including himself—would bring forth a newspaper called *The Pall Mall Gazette*, which would be written by gentlemen, for gentlemen, and conducted by men of reputation: university dons, club regulars, society insiders. He promised readers that only *The Pall Mall Gazette* would deliver the earliest news of English and continental politics—because its writers were part of the very circles they wrote about. “Why should the Gentlemen of England be unrepresented in the Press?” he demanded, as if the question had never occurred to anyone before.

Then, with modesty that was clearly an act, he described the literary and fashion sections—written, of course, by gentlemen who knew what they were talking about. He invited advertisers to join the cause, promising unparalleled visibility. He called upon the nobility, baronets, clergy, lawyers, matrons, daughters, homes, and hearths of England to rally round the flag of gentlemanly virtue. And when he finished, Bungay woke up from a second nap and declared, “All right.”

After the reading, the gentlemen discussed the paper’s management. Bungay sat quietly, nodding as if he understood every word. In truth, he didn’t. His opinions were simple: the Captain could write the best smashing article in England. If the Captain wrote a letter from Junius, or copied the Church Catechism, Bungay would still call it a smashing article. He pocketed the manuscript with satisfaction, paid for it, and gave little Mary a penny as he left.

Conversation turned to general topics. Shandon, in his usual jaunty fashion, regaled the guests with tales of high society—names, scandals, private conversations, and dinner parties he claimed to have attended. Pen found it hilarious that a shabby prisoner in a torn dressing-gown could talk so confidently about the great and the good. Mrs. Shandon, however, believed every word. She didn’t care much for society herself, but she believed her husband belonged there. Once, he’d been invited to dinner by the Earl of X—a fact she kept in her workbox like a sacred relic.

Eventually, Bungay rose to leave. Warrington and Pen followed, though Pen wished he could stay longer. He liked the family—especially little Mary, who preferred his lap to her father’s rough hand. As they walked through the prison corridors, Captain Shandon shuffled ahead in his slippers, already fiddling with his waistcoat pocket where the five-pound note had gone. At the gate, Pen breathed a sigh of relief—freedom, and Farringdon Street, once more.

Mrs. Shandon stayed behind, continuing her work at the window. She watched her husband race off toward the prison tavern, two men in tow. Dinner was ruined. A piece of meat and salad sat waiting, meant for her and Mary. But now, it would go cold. He’d be drinking, playing cards, returning late and drunk. Oh, the pain women endure!

She made tea instead. And oh, what a companion the tea-pot has been to countless women through the ages! It has comforted widows, cooled fevered lips, witnessed love letters, and held the secrets of lonely hearts.
