One could fill a book with scenes around the tea-table—Melissa whispering to Sacharissa, poor Polly weeping over her lover’s last letter, Mary bringing tea to her mother, Ruth brewing for her husband returning from the fields. And here, Mrs. Shandon and little Mary drank their tea together, while the Captain went out to enjoy his pleasures.

Jack Finucane, a fellow Irishman and Shandon’s faithful aide-de-camp, arrived shortly after. Jack always brought Mary a sweetmeat. He thought Shandon the greatest genius ever born, and he was always ready to run errands, settle debts, or collect money from publishers. Every embarrassed Irish gentleman, it seemed, had his own Jack Finucane—himself supported by others equally broke. Thus, Shandon marched through life with a ragged staff of dependents, all sharing in his misfortunes.

As they walked away from the prison, Bungay remarked, “He won’t have that five-pound note long, I bet a guinea.” And he was right. When Mrs. Shandon emptied her husband’s pockets later, she found only two shillings and a few halfpence. The rest had vanished: a pound to a follower, a leg of mutton and beer sent to a fellow inmate, a bill paid at the tavern, a dinner with friends, and a few half-crowns lost at cards. By nightfall, he was as poor as he’d been in the morning.

On their way home, Warrington repeated to Bungay what he’d told Bacon: that Pen was a fine fellow, full of genius, well-connected, and related to “no end” of peers. Bungay said he’d be happy to do business with Mr. Pendennis and hoped to see both men soon at dinner.

Later, over brandy-and-water at the Black Kitchen, Pen reflected: “It’s hard to see a man like Shandon—so talented, so witty—half his life in prison, the other half a hanger-on to a bookseller.”

“I’m a bookseller’s hanger-on,” Warrington laughed. “You’re going to try your paces as a hack. We’re all hacks, in one way or another. I’d rather be myself than Paley, our neighbour in chambers—who lives like a mole, underground and unseen. People throw too much pity on the poor writer.”

“But look at Shandon!” Pen protested. “A man of genius, forced to serve a vulgar slave-driver like Bungay—who fattens on the labour of others, enriches himself from their sweat, and can’t even speak the language of literature properly! It makes me furious to see a gentleman reduced to such servitude.”

“So you’ve started girding at publishers,” Warrington chuckled. “Bravo, Pen! What’s wrong with Bungay? Did he send Shandon to prison? Is it Bungay who’s spending that five-pound note?”

“Misfortune drives men into bad company,” Pen said. “We should be kinder to geniuses, especially when their very passion leads them astray.”

“A fiddlestick about geniuses!” Warrington snapped. “There aren’t nearly as many geniuses as people claim. Thousands of clever men could write articles, read books, and judge them as well as any critic. If a lawyer, soldier, or parson goes broke, he goes to jail. So does an author. If he drinks, he gets a hangover. If he orders a coat, he pays for it.”

“I’d give him more money to buy coats,” Pen smiled. “I suppose I’d like to belong to a well-dressed profession. I protest against that wretch—the middleman—between Genius and the Public, who steals half the earnings and fame.”

“I’m a prose labourer,” Warrington said. “You’re a poet in a small way, so you’re allowed to be flighty. Do you want capitalists to buy every manuscript presented? Every epic, every driveller’s novel, every tragedy? Who decides what’s good? Will the buyer get to choose? When Johnson sat behind a screen at Saint John’s Gate, too shabby to dine with the literary bigwigs, the publisher wasn’t doing him wrong. You can’t force a publisher to recognize genius in a ragged, hungry young man. Rags don’t prove genius. Capital does. And capital has a right to decide. I may think my horse is Pegasus—but the dealer wants a lady’s horse or a cob for a timid rider. My beast won’t suit him.”

“You deal in metaphors,” Pen said. “But you’re right—you’re very prosaic. Poor Shandon! There’s something about his kindness, and his wife’s sweetness, that touches me. I like him better than a better man.”

“And so do I,” Warrington said. “Let’s pity his weakness, and sympathize with his fate—even if he’d resent it. He takes his consolation along with his misfortune. He’s a prisoner, but not unhappy.”

“His genius sings within his bars,” Pen said.

“Yes,” Warrington replied bitterly. “Shandon adapts to a cage pretty well. He ought to be wretched—but he has Jack and Tom to drink with. He might have a high place—but he can’t, so he drinks with Tom and Jack. He might provide for his family—but Thomas and John want brandy. He might pay Snip the tailor—but John and Thomas take his purse. So he drinks, while his tradesman goes to jail and his family to ruin. Let us pity genius—and conspire against the publishing tyrants.”

“What! Another glass of brandy-and-water?” Pen asked, smiling.

Warrington laughed. “Video meliora proboque—I mean, bring it hot, with sugar, John.”

“I’d have some, too—only I don’t want it,” Pen said. “It doesn’t seem to me, Warrington, that we’re much better than our neighbours.”

They returned to their chambers, where two notes awaited them from Bungay—invitations to dinner, to meet his literary circle.

“We shall have a grand spread,” Warrington said.

“All except poor Shandon,” Pen replied, nodding goodnight. He went into his room, thoughts swirling. The day had stirred him deeply. Warrington snored loudly from the next room—deep, regular, the sound of a man who’d already given up on philosophy.

Pen lay awake, thinking.

CHAPTER XXXIV. In Which the History Takes a Turn for the Better—And Possibly the More Comic

Captain Shandon, under the relentless encouragement of his wife (who, it must be said, rarely interfered in matters of business unless they involved her husband’s imprisonment), had insisted that John Finucane, Esquire, of the Upper Temple, be appointed sub-editor of the soon-to-be-launched *Pall Mall Gazette*. And so it was done—by the ever-spirited proprietor of the Journal, who, though not exactly known for his generosity, couldn’t resist doing a favor for a man whose loyalty was as thick as Irish stew and whose gratitude was as loud as a foghorn at midnight.

Now, Mr. Finucane was no ordinary man. He was the kind of fellow who could hide a fugitive from justice in his chambers with such discretion that even the bailiffs eventually started showing up like clockwork—right on the stairs, as if they’d been invited to tea. It was in these very chambers that poor Mrs. Shandon would often come, veiled and trembling, to pour out her woes, her fears, and her desperate schemes for rescuing her beloved Captain from the clutches of debt and legal machinery. And when she left, Finucane would lean over the banister, peering down the staircase like a knight awaiting a dragon—or perhaps a rogue named Temple Lovelace, whom he fervently hoped might attack her so he could rush forward, break his bones, and earn eternal glory in the hearts of all decent men.

But this wasn’t just about heroics. It was also about meals. Many a dinner did Finucane provide for Mrs. Shandon and little Mary, who occupied a crib in the room and prayed every night that God might bless papa—and to which Finucane, though a devout Roman Catholic himself, always added a heartfelt “Amen,” as if he were personally guaranteeing divine intervention.

That evening, however, duty called. Finucane had an appointment with Mr. Bungay, publisher of the *Daily Journal*, to discuss the finer points of newspaper management—over dinner, naturally. He arrived at Dick’s Coffee-house at precisely six o’clock, having already cut his mutton (as Mr. Bungay phrased it) with the literary man of business, Mr. Trotter, and had spent the previous day outlining the entire editorial structure of the *Pall Mall Gazette* with the precision of a watchmaker and the confidence of a man who knew he was worth every penny.

He laid out the typefaces, assigned reporters to markets, turf, church gossip, and fashion columns (the latter being, he said, “a delicate art requiring both wit and patience”), and introduced Bungay to a network of informants so vast and intricate that the publisher began to suspect Finucane might have been running a spy ring on the side. But no—he was simply one of the best sub-editors in London, according to Shandon himself, and according to Finucane, who proudly agreed.

Finucane dazzled Bungay with calculations so rapid and complex that the publisher blinked twice and muttered, “Well, I’ll be hanged.” He then admitted to Mr. Trotter, “That Irishman seems a clever fellow.”

And now came the real purpose of the meeting: the liberation of Captain Shandon. Finucane knew, to the shilling, how much the Captain owed at the Fleet Prison porter’s lodge. He didn’t know *everything*—no man did—but he claimed to. He pointed out the Captain’s existing contracts, argued that confinement stifled productivity (though Bungay countered, “When he’s locked up, we know where to find him. When he’s free, he’s vanished like a ghost!”), and finally, with tears in his eyes and a voice like a foghorn, described Mrs. Shandon wasting away and little Mary growing weak from prison air.

It worked. Bungay, softened by emotion and possibly the second round of brandy-and-water, promised that if Mrs. Shandon came to him the next morning, he’d see what could be done.

And so, on Saturday morning, Finucane appeared at the Fleet Prison, dressed in his finest clothes—cheap but dazzling, like a rainbow made of rags—and with four pounds two shillings in his pocket—the full week’s salary from the *Daily Journal*, minus two shillings spent on gloves (which, he assured himself, were essential for gentlemanly conduct).

He gave Mrs. Shandon a wink so knowing and joyful that she immediately suspected good news. She hurriedly donned her bonnet and shawl, while Finucane offered to escort them on a walk—“for fresh air,” he said, though everyone knew it was really to escape the prison’s depressing atmosphere and perhaps avoid a sudden death by boredom.

“May I go, Charley?” she asked. “Or shall I stay? You’re poorly today, dear.”
“He’s got a headache,” Finucane informed her. “He suffers from headaches. I persuaded him to stay in bed.”
“Go along with you, and Polly,” Shandon said cheerfully, handing over Burton’s *Anatomy*—a book he used not for medical purposes, but for quoting Latin and Greek phrases in his articles, as if he were a scholar rather than a man who once tried to sell a horse three times before realizing it was dead.

So off they went—Mrs. Shandon, little Mary, and Finucane—through the prison gates into the free world. From Fleet Street to Paternoster Row is not far, but it felt like a journey across continents.

At Mr. Bungay’s shop, Mrs. Bungay herself entered through the private door, holding a paper parcel and a manuscript volume bound in red leather—containing, it turned out, a detailed account of her butcher transactions. She wore a shot-silk dress that screamed “I am rich and flamboyant,” a yellow shawl, red flowers in her bonnet, and a light blue parasol that looked like it belonged to a circus performer.

Mrs. Shandon, in contrast, wore an old black watered silk dress and a bonnet that had seen better days—like its owner. But still, she looked like a lady. Always a lady.

They curtsied to each other—Mrs. Bungay with the grace of a queen, Mrs. Shandon with the humility of a saint.

“I hope you’re pretty well, mum?” said Mrs. Bungay.
“It’s a very fine day,” replied Mrs. Shandon.
“Won’t you step in, mum?” Mrs. Bungay asked, eyeing little Mary with an intensity that nearly sent the child screaming into the street.

“I—I came about business with Mr. Bungay,” stammered Mrs. Shandon. “I—I hope he’s pretty well?”

“Couldn’t you leave your little gurl with me?” Mrs. Bungay asked, holding out a finger like a villain in a melodrama.

“I want to stay with mamma!” cried little Mary, burying her face in her mother’s dress.

“Go with this lady, Mary, my dear,” said the mother gently.

“Come see some pretty pictures,” Mrs. Bungay cooed, opening her parcel to reveal choice sweet biscuits—Bungay’s favorite after wine. Little Mary followed, enchanted.

The trio ascended the stairs together. A second biscuit sealed the deal. Mary chattered away like a bird set free.

Meanwhile, below, Finucane found Bungay in a mood darker than a thunderstorm. The previous night’s port and brandy had vanished, replaced by cold logic and a wife who had rebuked him fiercely. She declared the Captain was a wastrel, the *Pall Mall Gazette* a fool’s errand, and insisted he remain in prison. “Let him work there,” she said. “It’s the best place for him.”

Finucane pleaded, promised, implored—nothing worked.

But upstairs, the situation was changing. Mrs. Shandon, with her quiet voice and gentle manner, told her story—of her husband’s virtues, her child’s illness, and the horror of sending two children to school just to keep them alive in that “horrid place.” And Mrs. Bungay, though as grim as Lady Macbeth, melted.

She descended upon her husband like a force of nature. “Mr. Finucane,” she said politely, “would you care to join your friends in the drawing-room? I need to speak with Mr. Bungay.”

Alone, she delivered her verdict: “The Capting is an Irishman,” she said. “And I’ve always said I couldn’t abide them. But his wife is a lady. A good woman. A clergyman’s daughter. And a West of England woman—just like me, by my mother’s side. And didn’t you notice the little gurl?”

“Yes, Mrs. B., I saw the little girl.”

“And didn’t you see how like she was to our angel, Bessy?”

Mrs. Bungay’s mind drifted back eighteen years—to when she and Bungay were young booksellers in a country town, and their daughter Bessy had died just short of her birthday. Tears welled in her eyes.

“Well, well,” said Mr. Bungay, seeing her distress. “The Captain ain’t in for much. Only a hundred and thirty pounds. Half that will get him out. And we’ll pay him half salaries till he clears it. When the little ’un said, ‘Why don’t you take Par out of prizn?’ I did feel it, Flora, upon my honour I did.”

And so, with a heavy heart and a lighter wallet, Mr. Bungay announced to Mrs. Shandon that sixty-five pounds would secure her husband’s release—and he would advance it, deducted from Shandon’s future salary, provided she settled directly with the creditors.

It was the happiest day in recent memory.

“Bedad, Bungay, you’re a trump!” roared Finucane, leaping about the room like a madman, tossing little Mary into the air, and shouting promises of ten thousand readers a week for the *Pall Mall Gazette*.

Mrs. Bungay offered her carriage—“If I could drive you anywhere”—and off they went, two ladies and a child, with Finucane perched triumphantly on the back seat, while the driver threw triumphant glances at Bacon’s across the street.

As Bungay returned to his desk, he sighed. “It won’t do the Captain any good,” he muttered. “But Mrs. B. gets upset when she thinks about her misfortune. The child would have been of age yesterday, if she’d lived.” And he wondered, as all men do, how women manage to remember things like that.

But happily, Mrs. Shandon succeeded. She who had once begged creditors with tears now paid them ten shillings in the pound—and they relented. And the next Sunday marked the end of Captain Shandon’s imprisonment—for now, at least.

Thus ended a chapter filled with courage, kindness, biscuits, and one very happy Irishman who believed, with perfect sincerity, that he had saved a captain, a family, and possibly the soul of journalism itself.

On the appointed day, our two friends—Pen and Warrington—arrived at Mr. Bungay’s residence in Paternoster Row, not through the bustling front entrance where booksellers’ boys emerged like overburdened ants with sacks full of Bungay’s latest volumes, nor where hopeful authors stood trembling with virgin manuscripts ready to be sold to Sultan Bungay himself. No—this was the private door, reserved for Mrs. Bungay’s grand departures in her chaise, where she’d settle herself on plush cushions and glare defiantly across the street at Mrs. Bacon’s windows, as if to say: “You may have four children, Emma, but I’ve got a carriage.”

And indeed, Emma Bacon had no carriage—yet. But that didn’t stop her from throwing open her drawing-room sash every time Flora Bungay passed by in her chariot, holding up her four little darlings like trophies. “Look at these,” she’d cry silently, “Flora! This is why I can’t afford a coach-and-four!” And thus, armed with maternal envy, Emma shot arrows of pride into the heart of her sister-in-law.

As Pen and Warrington approached Bungay’s door, a carriage and cab rolled up to the Bacon house. Out stepped Old Dr. Slocum, heavy as a stone statue and twice as dignified, his equipage matching his ponderous prose—both resonating with authority in the literary Row. From the cab, two dazzling white waistcoats emerged, shining like new snow under a midday sun.

Warrington chuckled. “Ah, there’s Bacon’s dinner party too. That’s Dr. Slocum—author of *Memoirs of the Poisoners*. Hardly recognisable as our old friend Hoolan in that dashing white waistcoat. Doolan’s one of Bungay’s men—and look, here he comes!”

Indeed, Messrs. Hoolan and Doolan had shared a cab from the Strand, tossing a coin mid-journey to decide who paid the shilling. Mr. Doolan now appeared, arrayed in black, clutching a pair of enormous white gloves that he couldn’t help but admire, like a man admiring his own reflection in a puddle.

Inside Bungay’s house, the passage was lined with porters in evening coats and gentlemen in gloves so large they looked like they’d been made for circus performers. Hats and coats were collected, names bellowed up the stairs. A few guests had already arrived, but only Mrs. Bungay, resplendent in red satin and a turban, represented the fair sex. She curtsied to each guest with practiced grace—but her mind was elsewhere. Her thoughts were fixed on the rival dinner across the way, and whenever a new guest entered, she’d dart back to the window to watch the arrivals at Emma Bacon’s.

The sight of Dr. Slocum’s massive carriage, pulled by gaunt job-horses, crushed her spirit. On her side of the street? Only hack cabs had come. It was a quiet day for splendour.

All the guests were literary men—though unknown to Pen. There was Mr. Bole, the real editor of the magazine (Mr. Wagg being merely its nominal head); Mr. Trotter, once a tragic poet whose suicide attempts were legendary, now reduced to reading manuscripts for Bungay; and Captain Sumph, an ex-beau reader about town, vaguely related to Literature and the Peerage. He claimed to have written a book, been Byron’s pal, and was somehow kin to Lord Sumphington. His entire conversation revolved around Byron: “I remember poor Shelley, at school, being sent up for good for a copy of verses—every line of which I wrote, by Jove!” Or: “I recollect, when I was at Missolonghi with Byron, offering to bet gamba.” Pen noted that Mrs. Bungay listened to him with rapt attention—his tales of aristocracy thrilled her. If he’d arrived in his own carriage, she’d have bought every word he ever wrote.

Mr. Bungay moved among the guests with cheerful formality. “How are you, sir? Fine day, sir. Glad to see you, sir. Flora, my love, let me have the honour of introducing Mr. Warrington to you. Mr. Warrington, Mrs. Bungay; Mr. Pendennis, Mrs. Bungay. Hope you’ve brought good appetites, gentlemen. You, Doolan, I know have—always had a deuce of a twist.”

“Lor, Bungay!” said Mrs. Bungay.

“Faith, a man must be hard to please, Bungay, who can’t eat a good dinner in this house,” Doolan replied, winking and stroking his lean chops with his giant gloves. He made friendly overtures to Mrs. Bungay, which she rebuffed with icy disdain. “She couldn’t abide Doolan,” she confided later. Indeed, all his flattery fell flat.

As they chatted, Mrs. Bungay kept her eye on the street. Then—a magnificent vision: a huge grey cab-horse galloped into view. Behind it, a pair of white reins held by tiny white gloves. A pale face, adorned with a chin-tuft, peered out. An exiguous groom bobbed over the cab’s edge. The sight delighted Mrs. Bungay.

“The Honourable Percy Popjoy’s quite punctual,” she declared, and swept toward the door to greet the nobleman.

“It’s Percy Popjoy,” Pen said, peering out the window. And indeed, it was—the eldest son of Lord Falconet, known to us all—come to dine with the publisher—his publisher of the Row.

“He was my fag at Eton,” Warrington said. “I ought to have given him a thrashing.”

They’d clashed at Oxbridge Union debates, where Pen had clearly won. Now Percy arrived, hat under arm, face round and dimpled, hairless except for a sudden burst of chin-tuft—like nature had tried too hard and given up.

The temporary groom shouted, “The Honourable Percy Popjoy!”—which startled the young nobleman.

“What did the man want to take away my hat for, Bungay?” he asked. “Can’t do without my hat—need it to bow to Mrs. Bungay. How well you look today. Haven’t seen your carriage in the Park—why haven’t you been there? I missed you, truly.”

“I’m afraid you’re a sad quiz,” said Mrs. Bungay.

“Quiz! Never made a joke in my life—hullo! Who’s here? How d’ye do, Pendennis? How d’ye do, Warrington? These are old friends of mine, Mrs. Bungay. Say, how the deuce did you get here?” he asked, turning his lacquered heels toward her. She, suddenly respectful, smiled—now that she knew her husband’s guests were connected to nobility.

“What! Do they know him?” she whispered to Mr. B.

“High fellers, I tell you—the young one’s related to half the peerage,” said the publisher. Both men hurried forward, smiling and bowing, as if greeting royalty.

Then came the great Mr. Wenham and the great Mr. Wagg.

Wenham entered with his usual demure expression, eyes downcast, staring at the tips of his shiny boots—rarely looking up. Wagg, on the other hand, exploded into the room with a grin, his white waistcoat spreading like a fan, his red face glowing with anticipation of jokes and wine. He liked to enter a room with laughter and leave it with a punchline echoing behind him. Nothing could fully dampen his spirits—not even personal tragedies. When he saw a lord, he greeted him with a pun.

Wenham approached Mrs. Bungay with a smug smile, showing off his shoes. Wagg called her charming and marched straight to Percy Popjoy, whom he addressed as “Pop,” immediately launching into a story seasoned with what the French call *gros sel*.

He was delighted to see Pen, shaking hands and slapping him on the back. “How’s Baymouth? How are the Clavering folks? Sir Francis coming to town? Did you visit Lady Rockminster? Fine old lady, Lady Rockminster!”

These remarks weren’t just for Pen—they were for the whole room, proof that Wagg mixed with the elite. Wenham, meanwhile, whispered to Mrs. Bungay, showing her his boots, then smiled faintly. She admired his subtlety.

Pen, who’d read and somewhat admired Miss Bunion’s poetry (especially her description of herself in *Passion-Flower*, where she compared her youth to—

“A violet, shrinking meanly
When blows the March wind keenly;
A timid fawn, on wild-wood lawn,
Where oak-boughs rustle greenly,—”

and her mature beauty as something striking and captivating), was surprised to find her in reality: a large, bony woman in a crumpled satin dress, entering with a step as heavy as a grenadier’s.

Wagg noticed the straw caught in her skirt and reached to pick it up—only for Miss Bunion to beat him to it. She stepped on it, lifted it, and said, “Sorry I’m late, but the omnibus was slow. What a comfort to ride all the way from Brompton for sixpence.”

No one laughed—she spoke so simply. She wasn’t ashamed of her poverty. She wore it like a badge.

“Is that *Passion-Flowers*?” Pen asked Wenham.

“Why, her portrait shows her as a lovely young woman.”

“You know passion-flowers, like all others, will run to seed,” Wenham replied. “Her picture was probably painted years ago.”

“Well, I like her for not being ashamed of her poverty.”

“So do I,” said Wenham, who would have starved before riding an omnibus to dinner. “But I don’t think she needs to flaunt the straw, do you?”

“My dear Miss Bunion, how do you do?” he said. “I was at a great lady’s drawing-room this morning—everyone loved your new volume. Those lines on Lady Fanny Fantail’s christening brought tears to the Duchess’s eyes. I told her I’d meet you today, and she begged me to thank you.”

This tale—told smoothly—eclipsed Wagg’s claims of visiting country seats. Wenham now ruled the room. He continued with anecdotes of aristocrats, trying to draw Percy Popjoy into the conversation.

“I was telling your father this morning…” or “I think you were present when the Duke said…”

But Percy wouldn’t engage. He retreated to the window with Mrs. Bungay, watching the cabs at the Bacon house. At least, if he wouldn’t talk, she could enjoy seeing the Bacons jealous.

Then Saint Paul’s bell tolled—half an hour late. The party was complete, save for two guests who finally arrived: Captain and Mrs. Shandon.

After greetings, Pen and Warrington went to shake hands with Mrs. Shandon, who seemed touched to see them again. Shandon was smartened up—red velvet waistcoat, frill, wife’s best brooch pinned proudly. Mrs. Bungay’s kindness made her nervous—her red satin and bird-of-paradise feather now felt more like a crown than a costume.

“Nice-looking woman,” Percy whispered to Warrington. “Introduce me to Captain Shandon. I’m told he’s brilliant. And dammy, I adore intellect, by Jove I do!”

True—he had little of his own, but he adored others’. “And introduce me to Miss Bunion. She’s rum to look at, certainly, but that don’t matter. Dammy, I consider myself a literary man—I want to know all clever fellows.”

So Percy and Shandon became acquainted.

The dining-room doors opened. The party filed in. Pen found himself beside Miss Bunion and Mr. Wagg—who fled from the seat next to her, leaving Pen no choice.

Miss Bunion didn’t speak much during dinner, but Pen noticed she ate with gusto and accepted every glass of wine offered. Afterward, she confessed: “I took you for one of those Mayfair dandies. You looked like a little undertaker. And since I loathed the odious creature on my other side, I decided to eat and stay silent.”

“And you did both very well,” Pen said, laughing.

“Well, I do. But next time, I’ll talk to you—because you’re neither so solemn, nor so stupid, nor so pert as you look.”

“Ah, Miss Bunion, how I pine for that ‘next time’!” Pen said, gallantly.

But let’s return to the dinner.

It was a feast of “florid Gothic” proportions, Wagg whispered. “Men in creaking shoes and Berlin gloves moved like shadows behind the guests. Doolan yelled, ‘Waither!’—then blushed at his mistake. Mrs. Bungay’s footboy vanished among the crowd.”

“Look at that very bow-windowed man,” Wagg said. “Undertaker from Amen Corner. Attends funerals and dinners. Cold meat and hot, don’t you see? Sham butler. And I observe, my dear Mr. Pendennis, that wherever there’s a sham butler, there’s sham wine. This sherry’s filthy.”

“Glad you like it, Mr. Wagg,” Bungay said. “Glass with you. Got it from Alderman Benning’s store. Paid a good figure for it. Mr. Pendennis, join us?”

“The old rogue—came from the public-house,” Wagg muttered. “It takes two men to carry it off—it’s so strong. I wish I had a bottle of old Steyne’s wine here. Your uncle and I have had many a one.”

“How good this is!” Popjoy exclaimed. “You must have a *cordon bleu* in your kitchen.”

“Oh yes,” Mrs. Bungay said, thinking he meant a jack-chain.

“I mean a French chef,” Popjoy said politely.

“Oh yes, your lordship,” she replied.

“Does your artist say he’s French, Mrs. B.?” Wagg called out.

“Well, I’m sure I don’t know,” she answered.

“Because if he does, he’s quizzin yer!” Wagg cried—nobody got the pun. He was disconcerted.

“The dinner’s from Griggs, in St. Paul’s Churchyard,” he whispered to Pen. “So is Bacon’s. Bungay pays half-a-crown more per head. So does Bacon. They’d poison each other’s ices if they could. As for the made-dishes—they are poison. This—hum—ha—this Brimborion à la Sévigné is delicious, Mrs. B.,” he said, helping himself.

“Well, I’m glad you like it,” she said, blushing, unsure if the dish was actually named that. She suspected Wagg was mocking her. And so she hated him—with feminine fury. She’d have fired him from Bungay’s periodical, but his name was too big.

Warrington sat beside Mrs. Shandon, who wore plain black silk and faded ornaments. Her quiet smile stirred pity in Warrington’s rough heart. No one paid her any mind. She watched her husband, abashed among the company. Wenham and Wagg knew Shandon’s story—he’d worked with Wagg, was infinitely superior in wit and genius, but remained unknown. He drank quietly, taking whatever wine was offered. Bungay had warned the undertaker not to fill his glass too often. A melancholy precaution—made worse by its necessity. Mrs. Shandon glanced anxiously across the table.

Wagg, embarrassed by his failed pun, stuck to Pen for the rest of dinner, talking mostly about their neighbours. “This is one of Bungay’s grand days,” he said. “We’re all Bungavians here. Did you read Popjoy’s novel? It was an old magazine story by poor Buzzard, forgotten until Trotter dug it up. Bob wrote a few chapters, Popjoy allowed his name, maybe contributed a page here and there—and *Desperation, or the Fugitive Duchess* was born. The fun is to quiz Popjoy about it—he doesn’t know a word.”

“Glad you like it,” Popjoy said. “It’s a favourite bit of my own.”

“There’s no such thing in the whole book,” Wagg whispered. “I invented it. Gad! It wouldn’t make a bad plot for a high-church novel.”

Captain Sumph began: “I remember poor Byron, Hobhouse, Trelawney, and myself dining with Cardinal Mezzocaldo in Rome. We had Orvieto—Byron liked it. The Cardinal regretted being single. We went to Civita Vecchia—Byron’s yacht was there—and by Jove, the Cardinal died within three weeks. Byron was sorry—he rather liked him.”

“A devilish interesting story, Sumph,” Wagg said.

“You should publish some of those stories, Captain Sumph,” Shandon said. “Such a volume would make Bungay’s fortune.”

“Why don’t you ask Sumph to publish ’em in your new paper—the what-d’ye-call-’em—hay, Shandon?” Wagg bellowed.

“Why don’t you ask him to publish ’em in your old magazine, the Thingumbob?” Shandon replied.

“Is there going to be a new paper?” Wenham asked—knowing perfectly well, but ashamed.

“Bungay going to bring out a paper?” Popjoy cried, proud of his literary connections. “You must employ me. Mrs. Bungay, use your influence—make him hire me. Prose or verse—what shall it be? Novels, poems, travels, leading articles—begad. Anything! Just pay me, and I’m yours—right now, my dear Mrs. Bungay, begad!”

“It’s to be called the Small Beer Chronicle,” Wagg growled. “And little Popjoy’s to handle the infantine department.”

“It’s to be called the Pall Mall Gazette, sir,” Shandon said. “And we’d be happy to have you.”

“Pall Mall Gazette—why Pall Mall Gazette?” Wagg asked.

“Because the editor was born in Dublin, the sub-editor in Cork, the proprietor lives in Paternoster Row, and the paper’s published in Catherine Street, Strand. Won’t that suffice you, Wagg?”

Shandon was getting angry. “Everything must have a name. My dog Ponto has a name. You’ve got a name—and a name you deserve, more or less. Why grudge ours?”

“By any other name it would smell as sweet,” Wagg said.

“I’ll have ye remember its name’s not what-d’ye-call-’em, Mr. Wagg,” Shandon snapped. “You know its name well enough—and you know mine.”

The *Pall Mall Gazette* made a splash—big, loud, and rather messy—like a drunken gentleman tumbling into a fountain at a garden party. It was whispered that the paper had powerful political backing; great names were rumored to be lurking behind its columns like ghosts in a haunted library. Were these rumors true? We’re not at liberty to say—though we can confirm one thing: an article on foreign policy, widely believed to have been penned by a noble Lord whose face is as familiar to the Foreign Office as a bad cold in winter, was actually written by Captain Shandon, sitting in the parlour of the Bear and Staff public house near Whitehall Stairs. The printer’s boy had tracked him down like a bloodhound after a lost sausage roll, and there, beside his literary partner Mr. Bludyer (who was living in temporary exile from his own home), the noble Lord’s “masterpiece” was composed with a quill dipped in gin and a mind full of dubious facts.

And then there were the finance articles—those weighty, ponderous treatises that seemed to drip with wisdom and gravitas. Everyone assumed they came from the pen of a great statesman, possibly the very man who’d once said “I never read anything but the Times.” But no—it was George Warrington of the Upper Temple, writing between sips of weak tea and bouts of existential dread, who delivered those gems.

Was there a secret pact between the *Gazette* and some high-powered political cabal? Quite possibly. Percy Popjoy—son of Lord Falconet, a man so influential he could make a minister blush—was often seen creeping up the stairs to Warrington’s chambers like a thief stealing a sandwich. And certain tidbits appeared in the paper that only someone with insider access could know—like the fact that Lady Muffborough had left her gloves at the opera, or that Lord Steyne had secretly eaten a whole pie during a parliamentary debate.

Poetry also found its way into the *Gazette*, though it was poetry that looked like it had been written by a man trying to impress a woman while standing on one leg. These poems bore the initials “P. P.”—and yes, you guessed it: Percy Popjoy. His novel, which was less a book and more a series of fever dreams about love and politics, was praised in the *Gazette* with such over-the-top enthusiasm that even the author began to believe he was the next Shakespeare.

Mr. Pen, meanwhile, stayed clear of politics. He didn’t want to get tangled up in debates about tariffs or treaties—he preferred the quieter, more respectable battlefield of literature. And what a soldier he was! He arrived at the *Gazette*’s offices in Catherine Street, Strand, puffing like a steam engine, manuscripts clutched in his hand, eyes sparkling with the joy of seeing his name in print. It was like being crowned king of a tiny, imaginary kingdom where everyone read your words and nodded sagely.

At the heart of this literary circus was Mr. Jack Finucane, sub-editor and master of paste and scissors. With the focus of a hawk and the appetite of a bear, he combed through every newspaper in the land, hunting for news of aristocratic dinners, deaths, and scandals. If a duke died, Jack made sure the world knew. If a duchess hosted a dinner party with more than three courses, Jack would describe it in vivid detail—even if he’d never set foot in the place.

It was a sight to behold: Jack, in tattered trousers and shirt sleeves stained with ink and porter, eating a plate of meat from the cookshop while describing the splendours of a ball at Almack’s. He wrote as if he’d danced with the Queen herself, though his real life consisted of cheap beer and borrowed pens. Occasionally, he slipped up—once describing a lord’s carriage as “drawn by four magnificent mules,” when it was clearly horses. But the *Ballinafad Sentinel*, where he was chief correspondent, suffered more than the *Gazette*, which wisely kept him away from actual writing. Scissors and paste were his domain, not prose.

Pen, however, was another story. He took his reviews seriously. Armed with a fair amount of random reading and a sharp sense of fun, he produced articles that pleased both his editor and the public. He was proud—justifiably so—that he earned his keep with wit and honesty. And his work wasn’t just read in London: it reached Fairoaks, where the two ladies devoured each issue like it was a sacred text. It even made it to Clavering Park, where a young lady of refined tastes (and probably too many books) read it with admiration. Old Doctor Portman, who received the paper after memorizing all of Pen’s articles, declared that the lad had “spirit, taste, and fancy”—even if he didn’t write like a scholar, he certainly wrote like a gentleman.

Then came the moment of glory. Major Pendennis walked into the Regent Club, expecting a quiet cigar and a game of cards. Instead, he found Wenham, Lord Falconet, and other dignitaries gathered around a copy of the *Pall Mall Gazette*, laughing over an article that tore apart the latest travel memoir by the Countess of Muffborough.

The book, titled *Travels in Spain and Italy*, was a masterpiece of linguistic chaos. Written in both French and English (with equal confidence and equal incompetence), it was a treasure trove of errors. And the critic who pounced on it? None other than Pen himself. He danced around the subject like a jester at a royal court, mocking the countess with mock solemnity and impeccable manners. There wasn’t a single word that wasn’t polite—yet the poor lady was reduced to absurdity. Wenham grinned like a cat who’d just stolen cream. Lord Falconet laughed until he choked. Even Lord Steyne, whom Pen had never met, chuckled and said, “That’ll make the Muffborough writhe like a whale under a harpoon.”

And suddenly, the Major saw his nephew in a new light. “By gad,” he muttered, “the young rascal has some stuff in him!” He sat down immediately to write to the widow at Fairoaks, detailing how the great men of England praised Pen. Then he wrote to Pen himself, inviting him to eat a chop at the club and promising to take him to dinner at Gaunt House—because Lord Steyne liked people who could entertain him, whether by wit, folly, or sheer oddness.

Pen threw the letter across the table to Warrington. “Well?” he asked. But Warrington just smiled. Perhaps he was disappointed that Pen didn’t seem more thrilled. Or perhaps he was just saving his laughter for later.

Young critics are fearless creatures. They climb onto the judgment seat with the confidence of a lion tamer and the knowledge of a schoolboy. Had Macaulay’s *History* or Herschel’s *Astronomy* been handed to Pen at that time, he would have opened them, taken a thoughtful puff of cigar, and declared them “excellent works” with the air of a man who’d already read them ten times. With the help of the *Biographie Universelle* and the British Museum, he could summon up dates, names, and facts like a magician pulling rabbits from a hat—so much so that even his mother wondered where he’d learned all this. And when he re-read his old articles months later, he’d be stunned by how much he’d forgotten—and how little he’d really known.

Still, Pen had one ally: Warrington. Without him, Pen might have become unbearable. Warrington laughed at his pretensions, mocked his confidence, and kept him grounded. Shandon, too, enjoyed Pen’s bright flashes—though he preferred them to the heavier, more serious contributions of Warrington. But despite his occasional arrogance, Pen was honest. Too honest, in fact. Mr. Bungay, the publisher, grumbled constantly about his impartiality.

One day, the argument came to a head.

“Mr. Pendennis,” Shandon said, “what on earth have you been doing? Praising one of Bacon’s books?”

Pen blinked. “Praising it? Because it’s good?”

Shandon sighed. “Bungay nearly had a stroke when he saw it. We’re supposed to be attacking Bacon, not helping him!”

“But if the book’s good,” Pen said, “shouldn’t we say so?”

“And what do you suppose a publisher does with a critical journal?” Shandon asked, raising an eyebrow. “To benefit his rival?”

“To benefit himself, certainly,” Pen replied, “but also to tell the truth. Ruat coelum, let heaven fall—truth must be told.”

Shandon laughed. “And your prospectus? Was that mathematically accurate?”

“That’s not the point,” Pen said. “We agreed it was poetic license. The giant on the show-board is bigger than the real one inside the caravan. But we’re the fiddlers—we play our tunes. You’re the showman.”

“And leader of the van,” Shandon added.

“Well,” Pen said, “I’m glad my conscience lets me play.”

“But,” he continued, “we’re all party men in England. I’ll support our side with all my heart—after all, it’s my nest. And I’ll hit the enemy hard—but fairly. One can’t tell all the truth, I suppose—but one can tell nothing but the truth. And I’d rather starve than strike an unfair blow.”

Shandon shrugged. “When we need Bacon smashed, we’ll find another hammer.”

He didn’t say it aloud, but he thought it: *In a few years, you won’t be so squeamish.*

For Shandon, morality had long since gone out the window. He’d fought on every side, lied for every cause, and now felt no remorse. “You’ve a tender conscience, Mr. Pendennis,” he said. “It’s the luxury of beginners. I may have had one once—but it wore off with the rubbing of the world.”

“I don’t know,” Pen said quietly, “if some people’s hypocrisy isnches better than others’ cynicism.”

“It’s more profitable,” Shandon replied, biting his nails. “Wenham’s as dull a quack as ever lived—and look at the carriage he drives. Poor Mrs. Shandon will never ride in her own chariot. God help her.”

Pen left, thinking deeply. *Behold this man,* he mused, *brilliant, witty, learned—yet ruined by dishonesty.* And he vowed: *I will not sell my honour for a bottle. No, by heaven’s grace—I will speak only the truth.*

But fate had a cruel joke in store.

The very next issue of the *Gazette* featured an article that sent shockwaves through the office. Warrington read it aloud, roaring with laughter. Pen, working on his own review, froze.

It was a savage attack on the *Spring Annual*. And the person most viciously attacked? Pen himself.

His poems had appeared anonymously in the *Annual*. When he refused to review it, Shandon passed it to Mr. Bludyer—the man with the talent and the temper of a bull in a china shop. Bludyer, a genius of savage humour, tore the book apart with glee. He didn’t just criticize it—he destroyed it. Then, in a moment of literary madness, he sold the volume at a bookstall and bought a pint of brandy with the proceeds.

Pen stared at the page. He hadn’t expected this. Not from his own paper. Not from his own allies.

And yet, deep down, he couldn’t help but smile.

Because, after all, the joke was on him—and he was still alive to tell the tale.

Let us skip ahead a few months in the life of Mr. Arthur Pendennis—those uneventful, slightly tedious months that are always full of significance to the person living them but utterly forgettable to anyone else. We left him last, freshly installed as a professional writer—though Mr. Warrington, with his usual flair for the dramatic, preferred to call it "literary hackery." And so it was: a life not unlike that of any other hack—be he legal, literary, or clerical—whether stationed in a curacy, marching through a regiment, or slaving over ledgers in a merchant’s office. The routine is monotonous, the days bleed into one another, and the only excitement comes from the occasional deadline or the sudden onset of indigestion after too many oysters.

Now, let us be clear: writing for a living is no picnic. A man may have genius, wit, and a soul like a thunderstorm—but if he needs to eat, he must write. And when you're selling Pegasus by the pound, poetry goes out the window. Pegasus, once the noble steed of inspiration, now trots like a cab horse down Fleet Street, hauling reviews, essays, and half-baked editorials behind him. He pants, he sweats, and occasionally gets a crack across the rump from an irate editor who has just read a particularly bad sentence. It’s not glamorous. But then, why should it be? Genius, like any other profession, must pay taxes, wear waistcoats, and endure the occasional hangover.

Still, we ought not pity Pegasus too much. He’s no different from the rest of creation. If he’s whipped, it’s often because he deserved it. Let us thank our stars that George Warrington exists, for he would never allow such sentimental nonsense about “the sacredness of art” or “the divine right of poets to live in poverty.” No, Warrington believes that writers should work, pay rent, and behave themselves—just like everyone else. And so does Arthur Pendennis, though he’d never admit it aloud.

And so, with the *Pall Mall Gazette* firmly established and Arthur’s talents duly recognized (if not entirely appreciated), he set to work. Every week, he reviewed books—historians’ labors of love, poets’ carefully polished sonnets, actors’ latest performances—and did so with a certain flippancy, yes, but also with honesty and, when possible, good humor. Of course, some historians spent twenty years on a tome that Arthur disposed of in two days at the British Museum. Some poets had written odes so sublime they thought they’d earned their place in immortality—only to find themselves reduced to a paragraph of mockery in Pen’s review. The theatres complained bitterly. One actor, after reading Pen’s critique, declared he would rather lose a leg than meet him in a dark alley. But really, what harm was done? In those days, there were so few great minds that the critical world resembled a country fair: a few decent exhibits, plenty of clowns, and one or two prize-winning pigs.

Pen, meanwhile, remained modest. He knew he wasn’t Shakespeare. He knew he wasn’t even quite Byron. But he had a sharp eye, a quick tongue, and a friend named Warrington who could tear him apart in ten seconds flat if he ever started thinking he was God’s gift to literature. That kept him grounded. And that, perhaps, is why he didn’t become unbearable.

By these efforts—reviews, articles, occasional leading pieces when the paper allowed him to speak his mind—Arthur earned four pounds four shillings a week. Not a fortune, but enough to keep him alive, fed, and occasionally clothed in something resembling fashion. He also contributed to magazines and reviews, and rumor has it (though he never confirmed it) that he was London correspondent for the *Chatteris Champion*, which at the time featured some of the most brilliant letters from the capital—letters so eloquent, so vivid, they made one wonder whether the author had been secretly trained by Lord Byron himself.

All told, Arthur managed to earn nearly four hundred pounds a year—a remarkable sum for a young man without a title, a fortune, or a father to bail him out. On the second Christmas after arriving in London, he sent home a hundred pounds to his mother—as a partial repayment of the debt he owed Laura. Now, this act was met with ecstatic joy at Fairoaks. Mrs. Pendennis read every word of her son’s writings and believed he was the greatest thinker since Aristotle. She saw the hundred pounds not as money, but as proof of angelic virtue. She worried about his health, thrilled at his stories of grand dinners and famous men, and wept quietly whenever he mentioned seeing “Mr. Dickens” or “the Duke of Wellington.”

That winter, Arthur returned briefly to Fairoaks. It was a short visit, but it brought immense comfort to his mother. Helen had her son all to herself. Laura was away visiting old Lady Rockminster (a fact that remains suspiciously unexplained). The Clavering Park crowd was absent. Only Doctor Portman and a few old friends dropped by—respectfully, warmly, as if Arthur were already a man of consequence.

Between mother and son, there was nothing but affection, trust, and quiet happiness. It was, perhaps, the happiest fortnight of either of their lives. When Arthur left again, the house felt empty. His mother sent the money to Laura—though why Laura chose to flee just as Arthur arrived remains a mystery. Was she avoiding him? Was she jealous? Or was she simply tired of being the subject of gossip?

By now, Arthur was well known in both literary and fashionable circles. Thanks to his uncle’s introductions and his own charm, he’d become a fixture at dinner parties, club meetings, and evening gatherings. Bacon, Bungay & Co. welcomed his contributions; Mr. Wenham invited him to dine; Mr. Wagg looked upon him with approval. People said they’d seen him at the homes of noblemen, where he was received with warmth—not because of his wealth (which was minimal), but because he was pleasant, witty, and dressed better than most.

In London, reputation is everything. And Arthur had one. He was seen at one house because he’d been seen at another. He moved from Mayfair salons to taverns in Paternoster Row with ease. He breakfasted with a Peer, a Bishop, and a parliamentary orator, then dashed off to the newspaper office, where Finucane greeted him with the latest news from the Row. Shandon, the Captain, would appear with his pint of sherry (always silently delivered by the boy), and begin scribbling his lead article. Mr. Bludyer, the truculent critic, would roar from the front room, confiscate books from the publisher, sell them at his stall, drink the profits, and then proceed to “smash” the author in print.

Evenings were for walks with Warrington, dinners, balls, the opera, or quiet nights at the club with cigars and long talks. Arthur saw everything—the glittering and the grimy, the noble and the ridiculous. He didn’t realize how much he was enjoying it at the time. But later, when balls bored him, farces failed to amuse, and the best jokes fell flat, he’d look back and wonder: *Was that really me? Did I once care about such things?*

The London season was in full swing. Levees were held at St. James’s, carriages lined the Serpentine, dandy horsemen galloped up and down Rotten Row, and the clubs were packed with red-faced gentlemen reading newspapers. Everyone was in town. And naturally, Major Arthur Pendennis—who was *somebody*—was not absent.

On a certain morning, the Major sat by his fireside, feet submerged in a bath, sipping tea and reading the *Morning Post*. He wore a bright Turkish dressing gown and a bandana tied around his head. Without this ritual—without the bath, the tea, the paper—he could not face the day. Nobody knew how frail he was, except Morgan, his valet. And Morgan, bless him, treated the Major like a delicate vase—one that required careful handling, constant attention, and frequent polishing.

After the bath, Morgan dried the Major’s feet, helped him into his waistband and wig, starched his cravat, polished his boots, and placed his gloves with military precision. During this process, they conversed—confidentially, intimately, like two old comrades sharing secrets.

Morgan, it must be said, was not merely a valet. He was a man of the world. He belonged to two influential clubs in Mayfair and Pimlico. He dined with butlers and valets of the highest rank. He knew more about London society than most journalists. For while we might imagine that gossip flows only through parlors and drawing rooms, the real truth lies in kitchens, servants’ halls, and the back rooms of clubs.

Morgan knew everything. He knew which duke was broke, which earl was sleeping with his wife’s maid, which noblewoman was trying to marry her daughter off to a foreign prince. He knew who was in debt, who was cheating on whom, and who had recently been caught stealing silver from the family sideboard. And he shared it all with the Major—during the toilet, between the shaving and the boot-polishing.

That morning, the Major read of Lady Clavering’s presentation at court, along with Miss Amory’s debut. The descriptions of their dresses were elaborate, precise, and utterly baffling to anyone not fluent in the language of fashion. Seeing their names, the Major asked, “How long have the Claverings been in London?”

“Sir Francis sent away his old man,” Morgan replied. “Took a friend of mine—Towler, sir. Tall, red-haired fellow, though he dyes it. Used to be groom of the chambers in Lord Levant’s household until his lordship went bust. It’s a fall for Towler, sir—but poor men can’t be particular.”

“Devilish hard on Towler,” said the Major, chuckling. “And not pleasant for Lord Levant—he, he!”

“Always knew it was coming,” Morgan continued. “I warned you four years ago, when her ladyship pawned the diamonds. Towler took ’em in two cabs to Dobree’s. A lot of plate went the same way. You remember seeing it at Blackwall, with the Levant arms and coronet, opposite Lord Levant at the Marquis of Steyne’s dinner?”

“I beg your pardon—did I cut you, sir?” the Major asked, suddenly aware of the razor.

“No, sir,” Morgan said, resuming. “They’ve taken a house in Grosvenor Place. Her ladyship’s giving three parties and a dinner a week. But her fortune won’t stand it—can’t stand it.”

“Gad, she had a devilish good cook when I was at Fairoaks,” the Major remarked.

“Marobblan was his name, sir. Gone now,” Morgan said.

“Oh, I was sorry to lose him,” the Major said, genuinely.

“There’s been a tremenjuous row about Marobblan,” Morgan continued. “At a ball at Baymouth, he challenged Mr. Harthur to fight a duel—Mr. Arthur was very near knocking him down and throwing him out the window. But Chevalier Strong stopped it—I beg pardon, the holtercation. French cooks have as much pride as real gentlemen.”

“I heard of that,” the Major said.

“But that wasn’t why he was fired,” Morgan added. “It was Miss Hamory. He climbed the grapevine to her window, trying to get in—was caught. Mr. Strong came out, they turned on the garden-engine, and there was no end of a row.”

“Confound his impudence!” the Major exclaimed. “You don’t mean to say Miss Amory encouraged him?”

Morgan shrugged. “Know nothing about it, sir. Servants don’t know those kind of things. Most likely there was nothing in it. So many lies are told about families. Marobblan went away—bag and baggage, saucepans, piano, and all. He had a piano, wrote poetry in French, took lodgings at Clavering, hankered about the premises. They said Madam Fribsy, the milliner, brought letters to Miss Hamory—but I don’t believe a word. Nor that he tried to poison himself with charcoal—that was all a humbug between him and Madam Fribsy. Nearly shot by the park keeper, too.”

Later that day, the Major stood at the window of Bays’s Club, watching the world go by. There, walking down the street, was Arthur—with Mr. Popjoy.

“Look!” Popjoy said. “Did you ever pass Bays’s at four o’clock without seeing that collection of old fogies? They ought to be cast in wax and put in Madame Tussaud’s.”

“In the chamber of horrors,” Arthur laughed.

“In the chamber of horrors! Gad, doosid good!” Popjoy cried. “They’re old rogues, most of ’em. There’s old Blondel. There’s my Uncle Colchicum—the most confounded old sinner in Europe. There’s—hullo! Someone’s tapping the window.”

“It’s my uncle, the Major,” Arthur said.

“Notorious old rogue,” Popjoy said, wagging his head. (“Notowious old wogue,” he pronounced, making it sound even more damning.) “He’s beckoning you in. Wants to speak to you.”

“Come in too,” Arthur said.

“Can’t,” Popjoy replied. “Cut Uncle Col. two years ago—about Mademoiselle Frangipane. Ta, ta.” And with that, he sauntered into Blacquiere’s, a nearby haunt of younger sinners.

Arthur stepped into the club, where the Major awaited him—dressed, groomed, and ready to dispense wisdom, gossip, and possibly a mild rebuke. The world outside was changing. But inside Bays’s, time stood still—like the old bucks in the window, preserved in wax, forever staring at the past.

CHAPTER XXXVIII. In which the Sylph Reappears—And Also a Few Other Things

Now, if you’ve ever wondered how the rich get richer without actually doing anything (except perhaps sitting on their gold and occasionally blinking), then let me tell you about Lady Clavering. Her fortune was so vast that even Morgan, her valet—a man who’d once been paid to polish a silver candlestick with his teeth—knew more about it than any of the other footmen in London. When she arrived in town, the gossip mill went into overdrive: indigo factories? Check. Opium clippers? Naturally. Banks full of rupees like they were going out of fashion? Oh yes. And don’t forget the diamonds—jewels from princes who’d sold their souls for a loan from Lady Clavering’s father, Snell, back when he was still just a gentleman with a lot of money and a very good accountant.

Her bank balance alone had enough zeroes to make a mathematician faint. It was whispered that an envoy from the Nawaub of Lucknow—Colonel Altamont, a man who’d supposedly converted to Islam, survived a dozen near-death experiences, and once wrestled a tiger during a thunderstorm—was currently negotiating with the Begum Clavering (as she was now being called) over the sale of the “Light of the Dewan,” a diamond so large it could double as a dinner plate for a particularly hungry camel.

And so, before Lady Clavering had even stepped off the carriage, her fame had already reached the upper echelons of society. Which brings us to one of England’s greatest virtues: we don’t care *how* you got your money, only that you *have* it. If you’re rich enough, we’ll invite you to our balls, marry your daughters, dance with your dogs, and pretend you’ve always belonged. Our aristocracy may be haughty, but it’s also practical. Money is the ultimate equalizer—even if it does come with a few side effects, like blackmail, gambling debts, and a certain Colonel Altamont who likes to threaten people with newspaper exposés.

Meanwhile, our dear Chevalier Strong—gentleman, soldier, and general all-around fixer—had been busy making sure the Claverings’ new London house was ready for its grand entrance. He supervised the decorators, rearranged the sofas like a general preparing for battle, and even gave advice on wine selection—though he did sneakily stock his own cellar while he was at it. His rooms in Shepherd’s Inn were now the envy of every man who’d ever eaten a cutlet and wished it came with a bottle of claret.

And who should be sharing those rooms with him? None other than Colonel Altamont himself—the same man who’d threatened to blow up the entire Clavering family if they didn’t pay him a thousand pounds by Friday at noon. Yes, the very same man whom Sir Francis Clavering had been forced to see again after a run of bad luck at Baden-Baden, where the Colonel lost everything playing red—twelve times in a row. “No gentleman can stand against a colour coming up fourteen times,” he lamented, though no one believed him. The truth was, he’d drawn on Sir Francis’s credit, and poor Clavering—already stretched thin by election expenses and furniture bills—had no choice but to accept the bill, muttering curses under his breath and wishing the Colonel had stayed locked in a German debtor’s prison for life.

But here’s the kicker: Lady Clavering knew nothing about any of this. She was generous, yes—she’d give her husband a handsome allowance—but she kept strict accounts. She wasn’t the kind of woman to hand over blank cheques or let her husband sign bills without knowing what they were for. So when Clavering needed cash for the Colonel, he couldn’t speak to her. That’s where Strong came in—his personal banker, financial advisor, and occasional moral compass (though not always in that order).

And so, in the cozy, slightly shady world of Shepherd’s Inn, business was conducted in whispers and signed notes. Strong, despite having no money of his own, became the drawer of nearly every bill Sir Francis accepted. He’d discount them in the City, negotiate payment extensions, and keep the whole thing ticking along like a well-oiled machine—or at least a machine that occasionally sputtered and threatened to explode.

It’s a curious thing, isn’t it? A man who’s been in debt since he was seventeen, who trades promises for cash, never seems to truly benefit from prosperity. Just when things look up, the moneylender knocks again. But Ned Strong, bless him, bore it all with the calmness of a man who’d seen worse—like being shot at in Spain, or having his trousers stolen by a goat.

But enough about the Colonel, the bills, and the existential dread of debt. Let’s return to Lady Clavering and her magnificent new home in Grosvenor Place.

First stop: the dining room. Now, this room was decorated in what Lady Clavering referred to as the “middle-aged style”—which, she giggled, must be because she and Clavering were middle-aged. There were Dutch chairs from the 17th century, a carved buffet from the 16th, a sideboard made from church wood, and a brass cathedral lamp that looked like it had been rescued from a funeral procession. There were portraits, tapestries, armor made of cardboard, and a collection of saints that seemed to be judging everyone who entered. It was, in short, the most chaste room in London—so chaste, in fact, that it made you feel guilty for breathing.

Behind it lay the library—busts, books, easy chairs, and bronzes so severe they looked like they’d been sculpted by someone who’d never smiled. Here, Sir Francis smoked cigars, read *Bell’s Life in London*, and nodded off after dinner—unless he was at a club, punting, or losing money at a gambling house. It was the perfect place for a man who wanted to appear cultured but actually just wanted to sleep.

But the real showstopper was the drawing-room. The carpets were so fluffy that walking on them felt like stepping through clouds. Roses and tulips bloomed on the floor, bigger than warming pans. Chairs came in every shape imaginable—some so thin you feared they’d collapse under a feather. Marquetry tables overflowed with gimcracks, china ornaments from every corner of the empire, gilt daggers, and Turkish papooshes. There were nymphs by Boucher, shepherdesses by Greuze, porcelain poodles and ducks, and two cockatoos that squealed louder than a couple of angry nuns. A clock sang tunes, another boomed like Big Ben, and everywhere you looked, there were Dresden shepherdesses ready to assist with tea.

It was, quite simply, the most elegant room in London. The Romans of the Lower Empire would have wept. Louis XV’s marchionesses would have fainted. Even Mrs. Hodge-Podgson, wife of the great Railroad Croesus, would have said, “Well, I never!”

But Lady Clavering? She barely noticed. “I only know it cost a fortune,” she said to Major Pendennis. “And I wouldn’t advise you to sit on those gossamer chairs—I broke one the night of our second dinner party.”

“Ah,” said Blanche, the Sylphide, with a sneer. “You would have liked to see Mamma break a chair, wouldn’t you, Mr. Pendennis?”

Pen blushed. “I should have been glad to offer my arm if she needed it,” he said, bowing.

“Quel preux Chevalier!” cried Blanche, tossing her head. “I have a fellow-feeling with those who fall,” Pen replied. “I fell once myself.”

“And you went home to Laura to console you,” said Miss Amory.

Pen winced. He didn’t like talking about Laura. Not anymore. Especially not when the whole world seemed to know about it. So he changed the subject—praising the furniture with such enthusiasm that even Lady Clavering was moved to say, “No, don’t praise me—it’s all Captain Strong’s doing. And Lady Rockminster says the salongs are very well.”

“Lady Rockminster?” gasped Major Pendennis. “If she approves, you cannot be far wrong. She is the very centre of fashion and taste.”

“Yes,” said Lady Clavering, “she’s taken us up.”

“Taken us up?” Blanche shrieked. “Mamma, you mean *we’ve been taken up*.”

“Well, taken up, then,” said her mother. “It’s very kind of her. We’ll like it when we get used to it. Only at first, one doesn’t fancy being… well, taken up.”

She continued: “She wants to give our balls and send out our invitations. But I won’t stand for that. I’ll have my old friends. I won’t let her sit at the head of my own table.”

“Oh, mamma, vous etes incorrigible,” moaned Blanche.

The Major, however, declared he preferred quiet dinners. “A dinner’s best the second day,” said Lady Clavering, trying to recover.

“On the 14th,” she added, “we’ll be a snug little party.”

At which point Blanche clasped her hands and sighed, “Oh, mamma, you’re hopeless.”

But the Major, ever diplomatic, accepted with a smile. He liked the idea of a simple meal. And besides, he could always bail if something better came along.

As for Pen, he was still young enough to find a grand dinner in a grand house thrilling. He hadn’t yet learned the art of pretending he didn’t care.

Later, walking away, the Major asked, “What was that little quarrel between you and Miss Amory?”

“Used to be,” Pen replied with a dandified air, “is a vague phrase when applied to women. Was and is are two very different things.”

“Egad, they change as fast as we do,” said the Major. “When we took the Cape of Good Hope, there was a lady who threatened to poison herself for you. Three months later, she ran off with someone else.”

“Don’t get involved with Miss Amory,” he advised. “She’s forward, affected, and under-bred. And her character—well, let’s just say ten thousand pounds won’t buy her.”

“Ten thousand pounds?” Pen laughed. “That’s not much.”

“Not much?” the Major said, chuckling. “I wouldn’t pay her milliner’s bill with the interest.”

“You seem to know millinery well,” Pen observed.

“I did,” said the Major. “And the old war-horse, you know, never hears a trumpet without he, he!”—and he gave a rather superannuated leer at a passing carriage.

“Lady Catherine Martingale’s carriage,” he announced. “Fine girls, though—her mother was a thousand times handsomer.”

Then, turning to Pen: “With your looks and prospects, you ought to make a good marriage someday. And remember—reputation for a little wickedness? That helps. Women like a bit of danger. But marriage? Marry a woman with money. It’s easier to get a rich wife than a poor one. And it’s far more pleasant to eat a well-cooked dinner than a cold leg of mutton.”

“So you suggest I stick to the 14th dinner?” Pen asked.

“Stick to it,” said the Major. “Cultivate them, but keep them for dining. No more love-in-a-cottage nonsense.”

“It must be a cottage with a double coach-house,” Pen quipped.

His uncle didn’t recognize the line, but he liked the sound of it. Arthur Pendennis, it seemed, was becoming a very clever fellow—especially at adapting to whatever mood the moment demanded.

Warrington grumbled that Pen was becoming unbearable. But really, the young man was just thriving. His success, his charm, his newfound confidence—they all pleased his elder companion. Like a man watching a child at a pantomime, he found joy in Pen’s exuberance.

And so, as the sun rose higher and the world grew brighter, Arthur Pendennis bloomed—not unlike a flower in springtime—with all the hope, humor, and hubris of youth.

On the appointed day, Major Pendennis—whose social calendar was about as exciting as a dustbin’s—arrived at Sir Francis Clavering’s residence alongside Arthur, who had no greater desire than to avoid the entire affair. They were greeted by the usual suspects: Sir Francis and his wife, and Captain Strong, whom Arthur was delighted to see, though the Major scowled like a man who’d just been told his favorite soup was made of turnips. “Clavering’s d—— house-steward,” he muttered under his breath, as if Strong were some kind of domestic usurper.

But before the Major could fully seethe, Mr. Welbore Welbore—a gentleman so dull he seemed to have been manufactured in a factory for boring people—arrived with the solemnity of a clock striking midnight. Welbore, though possessing seven thousand pounds a year and a pedigree longer than a Christmas list, contributed precisely zero to conversation. He sat there like a stone statue, only more depressing. But the Major found comfort in such company: it was familiar, safe, and required no effort.

Then came the Dowager Lady Rockminster (who had her own reasons for being nice to the Claverings), Lady Agnes Foker, and her son Harry—our old friend, now slightly less awkward but still prone to blushing like a beetroot when near a pretty face. Mr. Pynsent couldn’t make it—he was trapped in Parliament, where duty, unlike wine, never makes you feel better. Miss Blanche Amory arrived last, resplendent in a white silk dress that clung to her like gossip clings to a scandal. Foker leaned over and whispered to Pen, “She’s a stunner.” Pen nodded, not because he agreed, but because he didn’t want to appear rude.

Blanche was extra sweet on this occasion. She held out her hand with all the warmth of a summer’s day, asked after Laura and Mrs. Pendennis, and declared she longed to return to the country. She was simple, affectionate, and entirely unguarded—like a lamb who doesn’t know wolves exist. Harry Foker, who usually spoke only when cornered by a fire hydrant, suddenly became fluent. He chattered about Taglioni, the Sylphide, and the mystery of how cherries end up inside jelly. (“How do they get those cherries inside? Do they feed the jellies?”) He even hummed the ballet tune, which brought a smile to Blanche’s lips and a sneer to Pen’s soul.

Pen, ever the romantic cynic, remembered that Blanche once said similar things to him—about singing duets, about music, about feeling *seen*. Now she was offering them to Foker. And while Pen didn’t say anything aloud, he thought: *How many men has she sung with since I last saw her?* But he kept quiet. Instead, he smiled and said, “I’d love to hear you sing again, Miss Blanche. Your voice is… remarkable.”

“Oh, but you liked Laura’s,” she teased.

“Laura’s a contralto,” Pen replied, sounding bitter enough to curdle milk. “And contraltos are often out of tune. I’ve heard too much music in London. It’s all too loud, or too fake. I only care for what I heard when I was young.”

“I adore English ballads,” Blanche said, eyes bright. “Sing me one after dinner?”

Pen, heart full of longing and jealousy, pleaded: “Please. Just one.”

But then she turned to Foker. “Shall I sing you an English song after dinner?” she asked, flashing him a look that could melt ice in Siberia.

“If you promise to come up soon,” he said, grinning like a cat who’d just found the cream.

“I’ll come up fast enough,” he added, already planning his next move.

He wasn’t picky about wine either. “I don’t care for much after dinner—I take my whack at dinner, you know. When I’ve had enough, I toddle off to tea. I’m a domestic character. Simple habits. And when I’m happy, I’m generally in good spirits—ain’t I, Pen?”

And thus, the artless youth prattled on, while Blanche listened with infinite patience. When the ladies retired, she gave both boys a little nod and dropped her gloves beside Foker and her handkerchief beside Pen—equal favors, perhaps, but Foker felt the slight edge of preference. As she left, her final glance passed over Strong’s broad white waistcoat and landed directly on Harry’s face. The door closed. He sighed. Then drank a bumper of claret.

The dinner had begun earlier than usual—because it wasn’t a grand soirée, but a modest gathering—and the ladies departed while daylight still lingered. From the balconies, they could see Kensington Gardens, couples strolling, carriages rolling past. The sun hadn’t set yet; it still gilded the statue of the Duke of Wellington, erected by the ladies of England as a tribute to their national hero. Inside, the dining room was open to the breeze, and passersby could glimpse six gentlemen in white waistcoats, decanters gleaming, fruit piled high. A little boy at the railing shouted, “Hi hi, Jim! Wouldn’t you like to be there and have a cut of that pineapple?”

Meanwhile, outside, a peculiar figure caught the eye of Jeames, the footman, standing pensively by the door. This man was dressed like a king’s cousin: chains, jewels, shiny boots, brass buttons, and wristbands that looked like they’d been stolen from a circus. His hair was suspiciously black, his whiskers a deep purple—Tyrian, possibly. He was clearly drunk, swaying like a tree in a hurricane, hat askew, eyes bloodshot.

And then—laughter erupted from within. Strong had just delivered a particularly vulgar joke. The man outside laughed too—loudly, strangely, slapping his thigh. He winked at Jeames. “Plush, my boy, isn’t that a good story?”

Jeames, whose job did not include dealing with drunken noblemen, muttered later, “A holtercation with a feller in the streets is never no good.” So he called out, “Pleaceman!” and pointed silently at the man.

Policeman X marched forward, Berlin glove tucked into his belt. “Now, sir, will you have the kindness to move on?” he asked.

The man didn’t hear. He nodded, grinned, waggled his head. “Not stare—ho ho—that’s a good one!”

“Move on,” X repeated, louder.

The man staggered back, squared up, and roared, “Hullo! Keep your hands off a gentleman!” Then he swore—something best left unsaid.

“Move on,” X insisted. “Don’t block the pavement.”

“Who’s to stop me?” the man sneered. “Looking at my friends, if I like. Not you, old highlows.”

“Friends, eh?” X said. “Move on.”

“If you touch me, I’ll pitch into you!” the man bellowed.

At that moment, a cry came from inside: “It’s that d—— Altamont!” Sir Francis gasped.

Strong, rising instantly, ran to the door. The scene outside was now a spectacle: a bareheaded man in a white waistcoat, a policeman, and a flashy drunk nearly fighting on the sidewalk. A small crowd began to gather.

Strong grabbed the man’s arm. “For God’s sake, come in.”

“Send for a cab, James,” he whispered.

They dragged the man inside—though he resisted, roaring, “That ain’t the door! That’s the dining-room door—where the drink’s going on! I’ll go and have some, by Jove!”

The butler blocked the way. Sir Francis appeared, pale as a ghost.

“I will have some,” the man roared. “Get us a bottle of the yellow seal—the very best. A hundred rupees a dozen, no mistake.”

Sir Francis hesitated. Then, with a forced laugh, said, “Well, Altamont, come in. I’m glad to see you.”

Colonel Altamont—revealed to be the Ambassador of the Nawaub of Lucknow—reeled into the room, triumphant, and sank into the nearest chair. He stared around, frowning, then smiling, hiccuping compliments about the wine. “Very singular man,” Strong said gravely. “Resided long in a native court. They get very singular habits.”

“Very,” said Major Pendennis, dry as dust.

Foker whispered to Pen, “It’s the man who sang the Malay song at the Back Kitchen.”

“Try this pine,” Foker offered Altamont.

“Pines? I’ve seen ’em feed pigs on pines,” Altamont said.

“All the Nawaub’s pigs are fed on pines,” Strong whispered to the Major.

“Oh, of course,” the Major replied, unimpressed.

Altamont then fixed his gaze on Pen. “I know you, young fellow. Baymouth ball. Wanted to fight the Frenchman. I remember you.”

Pen bowed stiffly. “I remember the circumstance perfectly.”

“What’s his name?” Altamont demanded.

“Mr. Pendennis,” Strong said.

“Pendennis!” Altamont roared. “Pendennis be hanged!” He slammed his fist on the table.

“My name is Pendennis,” the Major said, dignity cracking. “And I’d appreciate it if you didn’t curse it so loudly.”

Altamont turned. For a moment, he seemed to sober. He raised a hand to his forehead, dislodging his wig slightly. His eyes locked with the Major’s. They stared—two warriors, one drunk, one dignified. Then Altamont buttoned his coat, rose abruptly, and reeled toward the door, muttering, “Captain Beak! Captain Beak, by jingo!”

And just like that, he was gone.

Less than fifteen minutes had passed since his arrival. The guests blinked. Clavering looked terrified. “Do you know him?” he asked the Major.

“I’ve seen the fellow,” the Major said. “Deserter from the Horse Artillery. Got into the Nawaub’s service. I remember his face.”

“Oh!” Clavering sighed, relief flooding his face.

The cab arrived. Strong helped Altamont in. Coffee was served. The gentlemen ascended to the drawing-room, where Miss Amory played the piano.

Foker was enchanted. Pen pretended to talk to others, but Blanche pulled him to the piano by singing his own words—words he’d written years ago, now set to music. He stayed by her side, turning pages like a devoted servant.

Later, Foker sighed, “Gad! How I wish I could write verses like you, Pen.”

“If I could, wouldn’t I?” Pen replied. “But I never was a dab at writing. I was too idle at school.”

No one mentioned the chaos downstairs. Pen almost did—but then he saw Clavering’s face: pale, panicked. So he kept quiet.

The evening was dull. Welbore fell asleep mid-song. The Major, usually a fountain of scandal, sat silent, watching Blanche play. When it was time to leave, he rose and complimented Miss Amory lavishly. “Your daughter, Lady Clavering,” he declared, “is a perfect nightingale—a perfect nightingale, begad! I’ve scarcely ever heard anything equal to her. Her pronunciation of every language—begad, of every language—is perfect. And the best houses in London must open before a young lady with such talents—and, allow an old fellow to say, Miss Amory, such a face.”

Blanche stared. Pen stared. The Major had just praised her as if she were royalty.

They walked home together. Foker, puffing his cigar like a chimney, clung to them until they reached Bury Street. The Major wished them goodnight.

“And I say, Pen,” he whispered, pulling his nephew aside, “mind you call in Grosvenor Place tomorrow. They’ve been uncommonly civil—mons’ously civil and kind.”

Pen promised, though he wondered why. Then the Major’s door closed behind him.

Foker took Pen’s arm and walked on, silent, until Charing Cross. Then he broke into a monologue about poetry, regret, and Blanche Amory—his heart laid bare like a wet towel on a cold rail. All the way down the Strand and up to the Temple stairs, he talked—about singing, about her voice, about how he’d wasted his life.

And Pen, walking home, thought: *I may have lost her to a man who sings about pigs and eats pineapple like it’s a sacrament—but at least he’s honest about it.*

Ever since that unforgettable evening in Grosvenor Place—when the stars aligned, the candles flickered, and Arthur Pendennis vanished into the night like a man who’d just witnessed something deeply unsettling—Mr. Harry Foker had been in a state of spiritual distress so profound it would have made even a seasoned philosopher weep. And yet here was Harry Foker, a man whose wisdom had once been the envy of Oxford dons and clubmen alike, now reduced to trembling at the mere thought of a woman.

You see, this young gentleman, though raised on a diet of cynicism, self-indulgence, and a fair amount of gin, had always prided himself on being above such vulgar passions. He’d given Pen advice about love, marriage, and the dangers of sentimentality with the air of a sage dispensing truth from Mount Parnassus. “Love is a fool’s game,” he’d declared over brandy one night. “It’s for poets and fools—and those who’ve never met a real woman.” But fate, that mischievous old busybody, had other plans.

The moment he left Pendennis at the door of his chambers in Lamb Court, Foker’s world tilted sideways. Back at the Back Kitchen—the den of wit, wine, and questionable morality—he found himself utterly unmoved by the usual delights: devilled turkey, gin-twists, and Hodgen’s latest musical masterpiece, “The Cat in the Cupboard,” which, if you’re not familiar, was a horror-comedy ballad so absurdly macabre that it should have come with a warning label. Foker sat through it all, nodding politely, mouthing “Bravo, Hodgen!” as required by social etiquette, while inside, his mind was screaming, *She’s real. She’s alive. She’s got grey eyes.*
