That night, he retired early, exhausted, only to be tormented by dreams of Miss Amory—no longer just a name, but a vision, a siren, a full-blown literary heroine sprung from the pages of some romantic novel he’d never read but suddenly wished he had. When he woke, she was there, standing beside his bed in the dim morning light, whispering, “Here I am. I’m your princess. You’ve found me. Now forget everything else.”

And so he did.

His former life—a tapestry woven from billiards, brawls, and banter—now seemed as dull as a Sunday sermon. The French prints on his walls, once vibrant symbols of joy and freedom, now looked like relics of a past life. There was Mademoiselle Petitot dancing over a rainbow? Ha! What nonsense. That was nothing compared to the way Miss Amory smiled when she sang “Yet round about the spot, ofttimes I hover.” He began humming it softly under his breath, as if trying to summon her back into the room.

Foker had never been much of a lady’s man—well, not the kind of man who sought out ladies of high society anyway. He called them “modest women,” which, while technically accurate, also sounded like an insult disguised as praise. His mother was sweet but boring. His cousins—those daughters of Lord Rosherville—were worse. One studied geology (a subject so dry it could make a desert blush). Another rode horses and smoked cigars like a pirate captain. A third fasted every Friday and turned her wardrobe into a chapel. The fourth, meanwhile, wore a nightcap during daylight hours and debated theology like it was a sport. Foker had no patience for any of them. Drummington, their ancestral pile, was worse than a prison. He swore he’d rather walk the treadmill at Newgate than spend another weekend there.

Lord Erith, the heir to the earldom, despised him. Foker returned the favor with equal vigor, calling him “the nightcap of the House of Commons” and “the Speaker’s personal shame.” Their relationship was strained, especially after the infamous billiards incident where Foker poked his uncle in the ribs with the cue and said, “Well, old cock, I’ve seen many a bad stroke in my life, but I never saw such a bad one as that there.” The Earl played on, smiling angelically, but spent the rest of the night gasping for breath and refusing to speak to his nephew again. For years afterward, he avoided Foker like the plague—though he never quite severed ties, because, well, family loyalty and banking agreements are hard to ignore.

And then there was Lady Ann Milton—his cousin, his fiancée, and the future Mrs. Foker. Since childhood, it had been decided that she would marry him. Her parents and aunt had arranged it before she could even tie her own shoes. She was good blood, they said. Good looks. Good breeding. No money. But what did that matter? “Your mother’s blood, sir,” the Brewer once said proudly. “Your own blood, sir. Nothing finer in England.”

Harry, ever obedient, agreed without hesitation. “If Ann’s agreeable,” he said, “I say ditto. She’s not a bad-looking girl.”

“Not a bad-looking girl?” the Brewer replied. “She’s a beauty!”

“Well, then,” Harry said, “I’ll wait. But give me time. I want to have my fling first.”

And so he did. He filled his rooms with French art, actresses, dancers, and scenes of romance and passion. He hung up a tiny portrait of Lady Ann among them—just a simple round face with ringlets—but it looked lost next to Mademoiselle Redowa grinning in red boots or Miss Calverley in tight pantaloons, playing a page boy. It was clear: Lady Ann wasn’t exactly the star of the gallery.

But now, all that changed.

Because when Foker woke the morning after Lady Clavering’s dinner, it wasn’t Lady Ann’s face he saw—it was Blanche’s. Clear grey eyes. A smile that could melt stone. And that tune—“Yet round about the spot…”—ringing in his ears like a ghost haunting a haunted house.

He sat up in bed, wrapped in crimson silk, and sighed. His life, once full of excitement, now felt empty. Conkey Sam? The Worcestershire Nobber? What were they to him now? The French prints stared at him mockingly. Even Miss Calverley’s letter—calling him “dear Hokey-pokey-fokey”—seemed rude, childish, and utterly irrelevant.

Lady Agnes, his devoted mother, noticed his pallor. “Why do you go to Spratt’s so often?” she asked. “Those billiards will kill you.”

“It isn’t the billiards,” he muttered.

“Then it’s the Back Kitchen,” she guessed. “I’ve thought of writing to Mrs. Cutts—ask her to reduce the wine in the negus.”

“No, ma’am,” Harry said. “It’s not the Back Kitchen either.”

And so, with a sigh that could have shattered glass, he confessed everything—not directly, of course, but through a series of carefully crafted lies and half-truths. He praised the Claverings’ dinner, their furniture, their plate. He complimented Sir Francis on his post-dinner brilliance. He suggested inviting them to dinner soon—only two weeks away, which felt like an eternity.

And then came the coup de grâce: “We must ask Miss Amory,” he insisted. “I—I want to ask Pendennis. He’s very sweet upon her. Don’t you think she sings well?”

Lady Agnes hesitated. “She seemed rather forward,” she said.

“But she sings beautifully,” Harry insisted. “And she’s charming.”

So Miss Amory’s name was added to the invitation.

Triumphant, Harry embraced his mother and retreated to his private sanctum, where he lay on his ottoman, staring at the ceiling, planning how to meet her again. He’d brought back from his Grand Tour a valet named Anatole—a polyglot of uncertain origin who spoke every language badly but served Harry with perfect precision. This morning, however, something was different. Harry tried on pants of every stripe and color. Boots too shiny. Shirts too loud. He scented himself extravagantly. Then, with a blush, he asked:

“I say, Anatole… when you engaged, didn’t you say you could… dress hair?”

“Yes, sir,” Anatole replied, confused.

“Cherchy alors une paire de tongs,—et—curly moi un peu.”

Anatole, thinking his master was either going to a masquerade or falling in love, fetched the tongs from the old butler and the lady in charge of Lady Agnes’s hair. Within minutes, Harry’s locks were curled like a West Indian diplomat’s. He dressed with extreme care—jacket, waistcoat, cravat, gloves—and stepped out into the world, transformed.

As he prepared to leave, Anatole whispered, “At what dime shall I order the drag, sir, to Miss Calverley’s door?”

“Confound her!” Foker snapped. “Put the dinner off—I can’t go!”

“No,” he said, catching himself. “Hang it—I must go. Poyntz and Rougemont are coming. Drag at Pelham Corner at six o’clock.”

But before that, he had one more stop: Lamb Court. He drove down, passing Grosvenor Place, and paused long enough to peek up at Miss Amory’s window—pink curtains fluttering in the breeze like the wings of a butterfly. Satisfied, he continued to Pendennis’s chambers.

Now, why visit Pendennis? Why rush to see him after not seeing him in months? Why feel such urgency?

Ah, young ladies—if you’ve ever experienced the same sudden shift in affection, you’ll understand. When love strikes, people change. The friends you once adored become tedious. The places you loved lose their charm. But the ones connected to your beloved—oh, they become precious. You start caring about their family. Their sister. Their dog. Their father’s favorite cigar. You want to know everything.

And so it was with Foker. Pendennis, once merely a friend, was now the key to the kingdom. He lived near Miss Amory. He knew her. He’d seen her. He’d heard her sing. He was, in effect, the bridge between Foker and the object of his desire.

What would Lady Ann Milton have said if she’d known? Probably something along the lines of, “Oh dear. Not again. First the billiards, now this. Can’t he just be normal?”

But then, love is never normal. It’s ridiculous, irrational, and utterly irresistible. And poor Harry Foker—once the most cynical, jolly, fast-living gentleman in London—was now a prisoner of his own heart, waiting for a chance to see Pendennis, to hear news of Miss Amory, and perhaps, just perhaps, to find a way to steal a glimpse of her again.

And if you ask why he was so eager to see Pendennis, well, let me tell you: because sometimes, the best way to get close to someone you love is to become friends with the person who knows them best. Especially when that person happens to be a man who lives in the same neighborhood, has dinner parties with her family, and might, just might, know where she keeps her spare handkerchiefs.

Love, as they say, makes fools of us all. And Harry Foker, bless him, was now a fool of the highest order.

CHAPTER XLI. A Tale of Two Cities—Richmond and Greenwich

Poor Harry Foker found himself at Richmond, seated at a dinner table that might have been designed by the devil himself to test the patience of a man who’d just lost his last nerve. The meal? A masterpiece of excess: melting ices like tears of sugar, pineapples carved into shapes that made him wonder if they were meant for dessert or sculpture, bottles emptying themselves into glasses with no apparent purpose, and cigar ash scattered across fruit like the fallout of some minor war. And yet, despite it all, he felt nothing but despair.

“I wonder how I ever thought these people were worth my time,” he muttered under his breath, staring blankly at the chaos before him. “Rougemont’s got crow’s-feet like a weathered map, and her cheeks look like they’ve been painted by a clown who ran out of skill. Calverley talking slang? Ugh. I hate chaff in women—especially when it comes with a smirk. And Colchicum! That old relic, riding down here in his brougham with a coronet on top of it, sitting like a stuffed badger between Mademoiselle Coralie and her mother! An English peer, and a horse-rider from Franconi’s circus? It’s not right. Not right at all. I’m not proud—but this is beyond taste.”

“Twopence-halfpenny for your thoughts, Fokey!” Miss Rougemont called out, plucking her cigar from lips as red as a ripe tomato. She was clearly enjoying herself, while Harry looked like he’d rather be buried in a crypt beneath the churchyard.

“Does Foker ever think?” drawled Mr. Poyntz, leaning back with the air of someone who had just discovered the secret of life—and then promptly forgotten it.

“Foker,” said Poyntz, gesturing dramatically toward the young man, “here is a considerable sum offered by a fair capitalist at this end of the table—for the present emanations of your valuable and acute intellect, old boy!”

“What the deuce is Poyntz talking about?” Miss Calverley asked her neighbour, eyes narrowing. “I hate him. He’s a sneering, drawling beast.”

“Oh, what a droll little man!” said Mademoiselle Coralie, in French, her voice rich with the accent of Gascony, where sunburnt cheeks and dark eyes are born of heat and passion. “He doesn’t look twenty years old. What a droll!”

“I wish I were,” sighed Lord Colchicum, turning his purple face toward a large goblet of claret. “C’te Jeunesse. Peuh! je m’en fiche.”

Madame Brack, Coralie’s mother, took a generous pinch of snuff from Lord Colchicum’s delicate gold box. “Je m’aime que les hommes faits, moi,” she declared. “Comme milor. Coralie! n’est-ce pas que tu n’aimes que les hommes faits, ma bichette?”

“My lord,” she added with a grin, “you flatter me.”

“Taisez-vous, Maman, vous n’etes qu’une bete,” Coralie snapped, shrugging her broad shoulders. My lord, catching the tone, quietly pocketed his snuff-box—no doubt wary of Madame Brack’s fingers getting too close to his Mackabaw.

Needless to say, the rest of the banquet was a symphony of idle chatter, half-truths, and pretentious nonsense. Nothing edifying. Nothing memorable. Except perhaps the fact that not every woman of the corps de dance is like Miss Calverley, nor every peer quite so… eccentric as the late Viscount Colchicum. But history has shown us such figures: men who preferred the company of youth over their own age, offering their wisdom like free samples at a department store. And yes, there were respectable men—like Major Pendennis—who didn’t mind the entertainment so much as the *publicity*.

For instance, our dear Major would have gladly joined any party of pleasure—provided it was *en petit comité*, and included gentlemen like Lord Steyne and Lord Colchicum. “Give the young men their pleasures,” he once told Arthur. “I’m not one of those strait-laced moralists. I’m an old man of the world, begad. And I know young men will be young men—as long as it lasts.”

And indeed, he believed seventy years was a reasonable span for sowing wild oats—if you were a man of fashion.

That night, Mr. Foker drove his lovely guests back to Brompton in his drag. But he was silent, brooding, lost in thought. He ignored the jokes from his friends behind him, didn’t crack one of his usual witty remarks, didn’t even glance at the moon. When the ladies alighted at their door, one of them asked if he’d care for a drink.

“No thanks,” he replied, his voice flat, his expression mournful.

They assumed he’d had a quarrel with the Governor—or worse, some catastrophe had struck. He didn’t tell them the truth: that his heart was broken by the sight of Blanche Amory’s window in Grosvenor Place, lit up like a beacon in the night.

He sent the drag home with a groom and walked alone, hands in pockets, staring up at the stars. The moon watched him with quiet pity. And when he reached the house, he stood beneath the windows he imagined belonged to the object of his affection, and moaned like a wounded badger.

Policeman X, drinking beer at a nearby pub after delivering Lady Clavering from the French play, later reported seeing “another chap hanging about the premises”—a small man, dressed like a swell. “Looks like he’s in love,” the officer concluded, though he didn’t know it was poor Harry Foker, reduced to stalking the very person he couldn’t speak to.

And so began the great campaign: dodging Miss Amory through London like a cat avoiding a dog. If Lady Clavering went to the French play, Foker appeared in a stall—despite knowing only three words of French. He somehow discovered her engagements (perhaps Anatole, his valet, had befriended Sir Francis Clavering’s man and glimpsed the engagement book). At every soirée, Foker showed up—much to the surprise of everyone, especially his mother, whom he now ordered to apply for invitations to parties he had previously scorned.

“Why, I go to parties because it’s good for me to see the world,” he told her proudly. “And I go to the French play to improve my language. There’s no better teacher than a comedy or vaudeville.”

One evening, Lady Agnes saw him dancing—and complimented him on his elegance.

“Ah, I learned in Paris,” he lied.

Anatole knew better: his master had been sneaking off to an academy in Brewer Street, studying for hours each morning.

The casino hadn’t been invented yet—so dancing lessons were rare, secretive affairs.

Meanwhile, Old Pendennis rarely missed church. He believed it was a gentleman’s duty to support public worship, and also a fine opportunity to be seen. One Sunday, he and Arthur walked across the park together—Arthur having breakfasted with his uncle and now heading to St. James’s for a charity sermon.

“You know,” said the Major, “we’ll go to church, and then we’ll pop over to the Claverings’ for lunch. Lady Clavering loves being asked. She’s kind, hospitable—monstrous so.”

“We met them last week at Lady Agnes’s,” Pen replied. “The Begum was very kind. But I agree with you about Miss Amory—she’s a flirt, isn’t she?”

“She’s the most confounded little flirt in London,” Pen laughed. “She nearly devoured Harry Foker at dinner—gave him all the talk, though I tried to take her down.”

“Bah!” said the Major. “Harry’s engaged to his cousin—everyone knows it. A fine coup by Lady Rosherville. And Foker senior? He’s a parvenu who adores big men. He’ll make sure his son does as he’s told.”

“And what about Miss Amory?” Pen asked. “Is she really just a flirt?”

“She’s dangerous,” Pen admitted. “I was smitten myself once. Very far gone, too.”

“Were you?” the Major asked, eyes narrowing. “Did she return it?”

Pen laughed. “At one time, I thought I was pretty well in her good graces. But my mother didn’t like her. The affair ended.”

He didn’t mention the full story—the letters, the stolen glances, the near-kiss in the garden.

“A man might go farther and fare worse, Arthur,” said the Major, still watching him closely. “Her father was a ship’s mate, they say. No money.”

“That’s ten thousand pounds and a girl raised like her,” Pen countered, sounding dandified.

“What’s ten thousand pounds?” the Major retorted. “She’s worth more than that. From what I’ve seen—and heard—I’d say she’s clever, accomplished. Would make a fine wife for a sensible man.”

“How do you know about her money?” Pen asked, smiling.

“You seem to know everything,” he added.

“I know a few things,” the Major replied. “But I don’t tell all I know. Mark that.”

“And as for Miss Amory—if I saw her Mrs. Arthur Pendennis, I wouldn’t be surprised. Or sorry. And if you’re worried about ten thousand pounds—what would you say to thirty, forty, fifty?”

Pen stared. Then laughed. “Well, sir,” he said, “make her Mrs. Arthur Pendennis. You can do it as well as I.”

“Psha!” the Major replied, slightly peeved. “You’re laughing at me. And near a church gate, too!”

They arrived at St. Benedict’s. The bells rang. The congregation poured in. Carriages unloaded their fashionable loads. Pen and his uncle entered, ending their worldly conversation with the solemnity of a church door.

Arthur, always respectful in sacred places, felt the incongruity of their talk. But the Major? He was utterly unaware. His hat was brushed. His wig trimmed. His neckcloth perfectly tied. He studied every head in the pews—the bald ones, the bonnets, the flowers and feathers—with the same calm detachment as a man reading a newspaper.

Then Pen glanced toward the servants’ seats—and spotted Henry Foker, Esquire, lurking there like a guilty ghost.

Following Foker’s gaze, Pen saw two bonnets—one yellow, one pink—belonging to Lady Clavering and Blanche Amory.

So poor Harry wasn’t the only one bringing love into church?

After service, Foker rushed out first—but Pen caught up with him as he lingered, unwilling to leave until Lady Clavering’s barouche carried her away.

When the ladies emerged, they found the Pendennises and Foker waiting.

To see and to eat—these were simultaneous acts for the good-natured Begum. “Come to luncheon!” she invited immediately.

Blanche was particularly sweet. “O! Do come,” she said to Arthur. “If you’re not too great a man. I want to talk to you about—but we mustn’t say what here. What would Mr. Oriel say?”

And the young devotee jumped into the carriage after her.

“I’ve read every word of it,” she added, still addressing Pen. “It’s adorable.”

“I know who is,” Arthur replied, making a pert bow.

“What’s the row about?” Foker asked, confused.

“Walter Lorraine,” said the Major, nodding knowingly.

“Oh, yes,” Foker said. “Walter Lorraine died of measles when we were at Grey Friars. I remember his mother coming up.”

“You’re not a literary man, Foker,” Pen laughed, linking arms with him. “I’ve been writing a novel. Some papers have spoken well of it. Perhaps you don’t read the Sunday Papers?”

“I read Bell’s Life regularly, old boy,” Foker replied.

Pen laughed again. They walked off in high spirits.

After luncheon, Miss Amory resumed the topic. She loved poets, artists, men of feeling.

“Some passages made me cry,” she said.

“I’m happy to think I have a part of vos larmes, Miss Blanche,” Pen said, puffing up.

The Major, who’d read only six pages, put on his sanctimonious face. “Yes, there are some passages quite affecting—mons’ous affecting.”

“Oh, if it makes you cry,” Lady Clavering declared, “I won’t read it.”

“Don’t, mamma,” Blanche said, shrugging in French style.

Then she launched into a rhapsody about the novel—its poetry, its heroines Leonora and Neaera, its heroes Walter Lorraine and the young Duke.

“And what good company you introduce us to,” she said archly. “Quel ton! Have you spent time at court, Mr. Arthur? Are you a prime minister’s son?”

Pen laughed. “It’s as cheap for a novelist to create a Duke as to make a Baronet. Shall I tell you a secret? I promoted all my characters at the publisher’s request. The young Duke was a Baron. The Viscount was a commoner.”

“What a wicked, satirical, pert young man you’ve become!” she exclaimed. “Comme vous voila forme!”

“But I still like Arthur Pendennis of the country best,” she added, giving him her full gaze—both the tender look into his eyes and the modest glance downward, which displayed her dark lashes like fans.

Pen protested he hadn’t changed.

She sighed. Then turned to Foker, who had been silently sucking the crook of his cane, wishing he were half as clever.

The Major, thinking he’d discouraged Blanche by mentioning Foker’s engagement to Lady Ann Milton, was wrong. She became *more* attentive. Praised him, his mother, his pony, his watch-chain breloques, his darling cane, the monkey-head buttons on his waistcoat.

Then, having coaxed him into a blush, she turned to Pen.

“I’m afraid Mr. Foker is a sad young man,” she said.

“He doesn’t look it,” Pen sneered.

“I mean—we’ve heard sad stories. Haven’t we, mamma? What was Mr. Poyntz saying about that party at Richmond?”

“O you naughty creature!” the mother cried.

But Harry’s face turned pale. Pen smiled.

“I believe you’re just as bad,” Blanche said. “I believe you’d have liked to be there—wouldn’t you? I know you would. And so should I.”

“Lor, Blanche!” her mother gasped.

“Well, I would,” Blanche insisted. “I’ve never seen an actress. I’d give anything to know one. I adore talent. And I adore Richmond. And Greenwich. And I say—I should like to go there.”

And so, gallantly, the Major broke in: “Why should not we three bachelors beg these ladies to join us at Greenwich? Is Lady Clavering to be hospitable forever, and we make no return? Speak for yourselves, young men—eh, begad! Here’s my nephew with pockets full of money—his pockets full, begad! And Mr. Foker, who’s well-off—how is your cousin, Lady Ann, Mr. Foker?”

“Here are two young men,” the Major continued, “who allow an old fellow like me to speak. Lady Clavering, will you do me the honour of being my guest? And Miss Blanche shall be Arthur’s, if she will be so good.”

“Oh, delightful!” Blanche cried.

“I like a bit of fun too,” said Lady Clavering. “We’ll take a day when Sir Francis——”

“When Sir Francis dines out,” Blanche interrupted. “It will be charming.”

And it was. The dinner at Greenwich was exquisite. Foker, though he didn’t invite Miss Amory, had plenty of chances to talk with her—during the meal, on the balcony, and later in the barouche ride home.

Pen came down with his uncle in Sir Hugh Trumpington’s brougham, borrowed for the occasion.

“I’m an old soldier, begad,” the Major said. “I learned early to make myself comfortable.”

And being an old soldier, he let the young men pay for dinner. On the way back, he teased Pen about Miss Amory’s obvious affection.

“Beautiful girl,” he said. “Good looks, wit, spirit. And in strict confidence—she’s richer than people think.”

And thus ended the chapter—full of laughter, longing, and the eternal folly of young men chasing hearts they cannot claim.

CHAPTER XLII. A Novel Incident (Rewritten for Humor)

Arthur Pendennis, having been soundly thrashed by life at Oxbridge—both academically and emotionally—had returned home to nurse his wounds like a wounded poet with a bad case of existential indigestion. And what better way to heal than with literature? That’s right: Arthur, in the quiet hours between brooding and being mildly depressed, had penned most of a novel. Not just any novel—no, this was *art*. Or at least it was supposed to be.

The manuscript, titled *Leaves from the Life-book of Walter Lorraine*, was written during Arthur’s emotional adolescence, when love felt like a death sentence, money was a myth, and every sigh echoed like a dirge. It was Byron meets Werther meets Mephistopheles on a particularly bad day—full of melodrama, despair, and enough tears to flood the Thames. The hero, Walter Lorraine, was basically Arthur’s soul with better hair and worse taste in women. He’d read German poetry so intensely that he began to believe he was a tragic figure in a Goethean tragedy, which, frankly, made him rather hard to live with.

And yes, the manuscript bore the scars of its creation: coffee stains, ink blots, and one particularly dramatic tear-stain that looked suspiciously like someone had wept over a lost love while also trying to write a sonnet about said love. Arthur once claimed that reading it again was like revisiting a ghost town where all the inhabitants were dead, but still occasionally screamed.

One morning, while Warrington lounged in an armchair like a cat who’d just eaten a canary, Arthur burst into the room with the manuscript clutched like a sacred relic. “By Jove!” he declared, slamming it down so hard the teapot did a little jig and the milk in the jug performed a brief ballet. “This is what I wrote when I thought I was in love with Miss Amory! Can you believe it? I used to leave poems in hollow trees like some kind of Victorian Romeo.”

Warrington, puffing on his pipe with the calm of a man who’d seen too much, leaned forward with fire tongs in hand. “Ah, the classic ‘Amori’ play on words. Very clever. Let’s hear a sample.”

And so, with the manuscript now in the grasp of a man who clearly enjoyed wielding fire tongs like a weapon of literary justice, Warrington began to read aloud:

“‘False as thou art beautiful! Heartless as thou art fair! Mockery of Passion!’ Walter cried, addressing Leonora; ‘what evil spirit hath sent thee to torture me so? O Leonora——’”

“Stop!” Arthur shrieked, lunging for the pages. “That part’s about Lady Mirabel! My other flame! The one who invited me to her house last night after seeing me talking to Wenham and Monsieur Dubois, who probably thinks he’s Napoleon and has orders on everything.”

“Two flames?” Warrington chuckled. “More like two extinguished candles. Both beauties in your book?”

“Both, or near enough,” Arthur admitted. “Leonora is Fotheringay. The Duke is Magnus Charters—only less charming and more likely to charge you for a handshake. And Miss Amory is Neaera. By gad, I walked home from Lady Whiston’s under the moonlight, and suddenly I was back in my youth. I pulled out the manuscript, and—well, it’s not great, but it’s got *feeling*.”

“And if Bungay won’t publish it, Bacon will,” Warrington added, already knowing the answer.

“That’s how poets work,” Warrington continued, “they get jilted, they cry, they write a book, then sell it. All poets are humbugs. Once a man starts selling his emotions, he’s a fraud. If a poet gets heartburn from a good dinner, he screams louder than Prometheus.”

Arthur bristled. “But isnity a poet supposed to feel more deeply? You see things, you speak up. Paley talks for hours about legal technicalities. Why shouldn’t I write about my feelings? Isn’t that just as valid?”

“Not bad speech,” Warrington conceded. “But still doesn’t make you immune to being a humbug.”

“What about Homer? Shakespeare?”

“Names like those aren’t in the same league as you, sir,” Warrington replied. “There are men and men.”

“Shakespeare wrote for money,” Arthur shot back. “Just like us.”

Warrington threw down his pipe and muttered something about impudence before returning to the manuscript.

It was clear: *Walter Lorraine* was Arthur’s diary of youthful folly, rewritten as high drama. But Arthur hadn’t kept it hidden because he believed it would improve with age—he’d just forgotten it existed, buried under old surplices, battered caps, and memories of failed flirtations.

Now, after nearly five years of neglect, he dug it out and found it… well, not brilliant, but not terrible either. Some passages had real spark. Others were pure nonsense. But Warrington, after reading half of it and smoking two pipes, declared, “I can’t read anymore of this balderdash. But there’s something here. It’s got freshness. Like springtime before you’ve learned to shave.”

Arthur blinked. “What do my whiskers have to do with anything?”

“They’re curling irons, Pen. You’ve got to fake it now. You can’t recapture that natural blush. The bloom’s gone.”

“Then let’s burn it!” Arthur declared, flinging the manuscript into the fireplace.

But alas, the fire had long since given up the ghost. It had boiled the kettle and retired. Warrington, grinning, retrieved the manuscript from the cold ashes with the tongs.

“Oh, Pen,” he sighed, “you’re a clumsy humbug. I saw you check the fire first. No burning. We’ll sell it.”

“To Bacon or Bungay?” Arthur asked, eyes wide.

“To both. We’ll play them off against each other. We’ll fatten the lad, give him a bath, curl his hair, and sell him for a hundred piasters. The rubbish is saleable. Take it home, edit it, add comedy, satire, maybe a bit of slapstick. Then we’ll take it to market.”

Arthur beamed. “Do you really think so?”

“You silly young fool,” Warrington said kindly, “I think it’s uncommonly clever. So do you.” And with that, he gently tapped Arthur on the cheek with the manuscript.

Arthur turned crimson—like a tomato after a very awkward moment—and grasped Warrington’s hand. “Thank you,” he said, voice trembling. Then he retreated to his room, re-read the whole thing, rewrote large chunks, added jokes, cut the melodrama, and finally shaped *Walter Lorraine* into something resembling a book people might actually want to read.

Meanwhile, Warrington, ever the master manipulator, subtly planted rumors among the publishing world. He told Percy Popjoy—gentleman of modest means and generous heart—that Arthur was writing “the most exciting novel of the season,” full of wit, pathos, and “every conceivable good quality.” Popjoy relayed this to Mrs. Bungay, who, though she didn’t understand novels any more than she understood algebra, trusted her husband’s instincts.

Bungay, bless him, knew nothing about books. He didn’t read them. He paid for them. He based his decisions on Mrs. Bungay and his readers. When he heard Arthur was working on a novel, he invited him to dinner—likely hoping to close a deal without even opening the manuscript.

Bacon, hearing of this, panicked. “We must beat them!” he declared. His reader, Mr. Hack, went undercover and discovered the truth: Arthur was negotiating with Bungay. So Bacon marched up the stairs of Lamb Court—where Arthur and Warrington lived in rooms so shabby they made a pawnshop look luxurious.

The carpet was ragged, the chairs broke under weight, and the smell of tobacco clung to the walls like a persistent ex-lover. Bacon tripped over a bucket, sat down on a chair that collapsed beneath him, and stared around in horror.

“Is this elegant?” Warrington asked, laughing. “Would you like any of these furnishings for Mrs. Bacon’s drawing-room?”

Bacon, confused, couldn’t tell if Warrington was joking. “I never can make that chap out,” he muttered.

But then he saw the invitations on the breakfast table: the Marchioness of Steyne would receive Arthur Pendennis. Another noblewoman was hosting a ball. Bacon blinked. This wasn’t just some struggling writer—it was a man of fashion, living in squalor.

“Ah,” Warrington said, “Pendennis is one of the most affable young men I know. Dines with everyone, yet eats mutton chops with us. Nothing like the old English gentleman.”

“Oh no,” Bacon agreed, slightly impressed.

“And you wonder why he lives here?” Warrington added. “Well, it’s a queer taste. But we’re fond of each other. I can’t afford a big house, so he stays with me. He’s a man who can afford to live anywhere.”

“I fancy it don’t cost him much here,” Bacon thought.

And then Arthur entered, looking like a man who’d just survived a war and was now ready to sell the spoils.

Bacon got straight to business: “I hear you’ve a novel. I’d like to see it. What’s your price?”

Arthur, playing coy, said he was already in talks with Bungay. This piqued Bacon’s interest. Offers flew. Vague promises of “liberal terms” and “great advances” were made. Arthur, feeling like a king, imagined himself buying a horse, a cab, and possibly a new set of clothes.

But let’s not get ahead of ourselves. I won’t say how much Arthur actually got. Because if I did, every aspiring novelist would quit their jobs and start writing tragedies about unrequited love. Let me just say: it was enough to make him consider moving to the first floor of Lamb Court, getting a brougham, or opening a bank account.

Major Pendennis, upon hearing the news, nearly exploded. “Who the deuce reads this kind of thing?” he muttered. “I never read novels. Except Paul de Kock—he makes me laugh. But Pen’s lucky. He could write one a month. Twelve a year. In four years, he’ll be rich. Meanwhile, I suggest he live properly. Get respectable apartments. Keep a brougham.”

Arthur laughed and told Warrington. But Warrington, ever wise, advised caution. “Be grateful. Don’t go wild. Pay back Laura.”

So Arthur did. He wrote a letter to the widow Portman and Laura, expressing heartfelt thanks and enclosing a payment that nearly cleared his debt. The letter was simple, sincere, and full of gratitude.

When Dr. Portman read it—especially the part where Arthur thanked Heaven for his fortune and kind friends—his voice cracked. His eyes watered behind his spectacles. He finished reading, folded the letter, handed it back, and then—after a moment of silence—drew Mrs. Pendennis toward him and kissed her.

Helen, the daughter, burst into tears on his shoulder. The Doctor, blushing furiously, led her to the sofa and murmured, in a voice barely above a whisper, lines from a great poet: “And the widow’s heart sang for joy.”

And thus, Arthur Pendennis, through a mix of talent, luck, manipulation, and a little help from his friends, became a published author—not because he was perfect, but because sometimes, even the most ridiculous dreams can be sold for cash.

CHAPTER XLIII. Alsatia Bred up, like a bailiff or a shabby attorney, about the purlieus of the Inns of Court, Shepherd’s Inn is always to be found in the close neighbourhood of Lincoln’s-Inn Fields and the Temple. Tucked away behind the soot-stained gables and chimney-stacks of Wych Street, Holywell Street, and Chancery Lane, it lies hidden from the outer world—accessible only by winding alleys where sunlight never quite dares to linger. Here, slop-sellers, brandy-ball hawkers, hard-bake purveyors, theatrical print vendors, and dealers in second-hand furniture (which looks as though it might have been used for something other than sleeping) line the narrow walls with their wares. The doors are many-belled; dirty children swarm around steps and shellfish trays, their voices rising above the clatter of pattens on damp pavement and the ever-present mud that seems to ooze from the earth itself.

Ballad-singers arrive with guttural cries, belting out satirical ditties against the Whig administration, bishops, dignified clergy, and even the German relatives of the royal family. Punch sets up his tiny theatre, confident of an audience—and sometimes a halfpenny or two. Women shriek at their children for loitering in the gutter, or worse still, at husbands staggering home from the gin-shop. It's a ceaseless din, a whirlwind of life, until you step into the quiet, old-fashioned quadrangle of Shepherd’s Inn.

At the centre, a mangy patch of grass hosts the statue of Shepherd, guarded by iron railings from the occasional assault of over-enthusiastic boys. The hall, bearing the founder’s arms, lines one side of the square; tall, ancient chambers stretch along the others, and a central archway leads into Oldcastle Street and beyond into the great London thoroughfare. Once perhaps inhabited by lawyers, the Inn now welcomes all comers—the legal profession having long since moved on. No major firms occupy these chambers anymore.

Instead, we find the offices of the Polwheedle and Tredyddlum Copper Mines, which have yet to surface from under water, and the Registry of Patent Inventions and Union of Genius and Capital Company—a name that sounds like a joke but is taken seriously by its clerks. The only gentleman whose name appears both here and in the “Law List” is Mr. Campion, who sports a fine pair of mustachios and arrives twice or thrice a week in his cab. At his West End offices in Curzon Street, Mayfair, he is known as Mr. Somerset Campion, where Mrs. Campion entertains nobility and gentry whom her husband lends money to. But here, he’s simply Campion and Co., and the same tuft of hair adorns both his chin and the firm’s official card.

It’s a sight to behold when his cab rolls up: the horse, decked in harness blazing with heraldic emblems, snorts froth from his nostrils as he chafes beneath the shining bit. The groom’s reins and breeches gleam white as snow, casting a kind of artificial sunshine over the otherwise gloomy courtyard. Captain Costigan has watched this spectacle many times, trailing through the court in carpet slippers and dressing-gown, his old hat cocked jauntily over one eye. He suns himself after breakfast when the weather permits, visits the porter’s lodge, pats the heads of the children, and chats with Mrs. Bolton about theatres and her daughter Leedy Mirabel.

Mrs. Bolton once danced at the Wells Theatre—she was the thirteenth of Mr. Serle’s forty pupils—and still remembers those days with pride. Costigan lives on the third floor at No. 4, in rooms once occupied by Mr. Podmore, whose name remains on the door—though most doors in Shepherd’s Inn bear names not their own. When Charley Podmore (a pleasing tenor, T.R.D.L., and star of the Back Kitchen Concert Rooms) married and moved to Lambeth, he left the chambers to Mr. Bows and Captain Costigan, who now share them. On fine days, you may hear the soft strains of Mr. Bows’s piano drifting through open windows—as he practices for fun or teaches his theatrical pupils, including Fanny Bolton, the porteress’s daughter.

Fanny has a good voice, a pretty face, and dreams of following in her mother’s footsteps. She keeps house for Messrs. Costigan and Bows, making beds, preparing meals, and listening to stories—while receiving music lessons in return. Though she thinks the Captain’s drinking habit is unfortunate (and suspects it’s common among fashionable men), she considers him the finest gentleman in the world, believes every word of his tales, and adores Mr. Bows too. And Bows, for his part, has developed a fatherly fondness for her—he can’t help loving someone, really. His heart is full of kindness, and he feels uneasy unless he’s doing so.

Costigan has seen carriages of distinction stop before his humble door. Listen to him talk in the morning, and you’d swear Sir Charles and Lady Mirabel were regular visitors, bringing the select nobility to see “the old man, the honest old half-pay Captain, poor old Jack Costigan,” as he calls himself. The truth? Lady Mirabel once left her husband’s card stuck in the little mirror over the mantelpiece at No. 4, where it’s remained for months. She did visit him personally—once upon a time—but not lately.

After her marriage, she settled a small pension on her father, who occasionally dined with her and Sir Charles. At first, Cos behaved with admirable solemnity—“in the hoight of poloit societee,” as he put it. He dressed in his best clothes, chose the longest words in his vocabulary, and addressed everyone with exaggerated courtesy. “Was your Leedyship in the Pork to dee?” he’d ask. “I looked for your equipage in veen:—the poor old man was not gratified by the soight of his daughther’s choriot.” He’d mention seeing Sir Chorlus at the Levee, recall past duties at Dublin Castle, and thank the footman for a “little dthrop more champeane.”

He was magnificent in his manners—addressing not just guests but servants, who struggled to keep straight faces while serving him. For the first few visits, he stayed sober, saving his revelry for the Back Kitchen, where he’d boast about Sir Charles’s wine and food until his sixth tumbler of whisky-punch made speech impossible.

But familiarity bred contempt—or rather, drunkenness. Soon, poor Cos began to disgrace himself at Sir Charles’s table, falling into premature inebriation. A carriage would be summoned, the hospitable door shut firmly behind him. Often, he lamented to friends at the Kitchen how much he resembled King Lear—how he had a thankless child, bedad—how he was a pore worn-out lonely old man, driven to drink by ingratitude, seeking solace in punch.

It’s painful to record such weaknesses, but we must also note that when his credit ran dry and his funds vanished, Costigan didn’t hesitate to beg from his daughter—sometimes telling less-than-truthful stories. Once, a bailiff was about to take him to prison, and he wrote: “Unless the—to you insignificant—sum of three pound five can be forthcoming to liberate a poor man’s grey hairs from gaol.” Lady Mirabel sent the money—with a stern warning to be more economical.

On another occasion, he broke a plate-glass window in the Strand and was held liable. Again, Lady Mirabel paid. Her servant delivered the funds to the Captain’s slipshod aide-de-camp, who then took a cab to the Roscius Head in Harlequin Yard, Drury Lane. There, the Captain was indeed in pawn—and enjoying several glasses of rum-and-water with his staff.

A third time, he claimed illness and needed money for a physician. Alarmed, Lady Mirabel drove to Shepherd’s Inn herself. At the gate, the porteress curtsied and pointed: “No. 4, third floor, name of Podmore over the door.” She entered, climbed the dingy stairs, and found her father opening the door in shirt-sleeves, ready to grill mutton-chops. Not exactly the image of a sick man.

And poor Sir Charles! He suffered constant letters addressed to him at Brookes’s, announcing that Captain Costigan waited in the hall. Or when playing whist at the Travellers’, he’d have to dash out of his brougham, lest the Captain seize him. Worse, he’d walk across Pall Mall, wearing that dreadful cocked hat, eyes fixed on the club windows.

Sir Charles was weak, elderly, prone to fits. He cried to his wife—whom he adored with senile devotion—about the horror of seeing his father-in-law again. He insisted he must go abroad, live in the country, or he’d die. Only after a second visit, where Lady Mirabel threatened to cut off his allowance entirely if he continued pestering her husband, did peace return.

She also rebuked Mr. Bows sharply: “Papa’s conduct is bringing me to the grave,” she said (though she looked perfectly healthy). “And you, as an old man, Mr. Bows, and one who pretended to care for us, ought to be ashamed of abetting him.”

That was the thanks honest Bows received for years of friendship and devotion. Yet, perhaps he wasn’t worse off than many others. Life has its burdens.

On the second floor of the next house, No. 3, lived two more acquaintances: Colonel Altamont, agent to the Nawaub of Lucknow, and Captain Chevalier Edward Strong. Their door bore no name. Strong preferred privacy—his cards listed a Jermyn Street hotel. As for Altamont, he wasn’t officially accredited to any court, merely on a confidential mission independent of the East India Company or Board of Control.

“In fact,” Strong explained, “Colonel Altamont’s goal is financial—selling some of the crown jewels of Lucknow. He doesn’t wish to report to India House or Cannon Row, but rather negotiate privately with capitalists, with whom he’s done important business both here and abroad.”

Since Sir Francis Clavering arrived in London, Strong’s chambers had become quite comfortable. He could proudly tell visitors that few retired captains lived as snugly as he in his crib in Shepherd’s Inn.

Three rooms below: the office where Strong conducted his mysterious affairs (still furnished with desks and railings from previous tenants), his bedroom, and sitting-room. A private stair led to two upper apartments—one occupied by Colonel Altamont, the other serving as kitchen and bedroom for Mr. Grady, the attendant.

These rooms were level with those of Bows and Costigan next door. From Grady’s kitchen window came fragrant aromas—Grady excelled at Irish stew, Altamont at pilau and curry, and Strong? Well, Strong could cook anything. French dishes, Spanish stews, fricassees, omelettes—all to perfection. And when his purse was full, he was the most hospitable man in England. He could give a friend a good dinner, a good glass of wine, and a good song afterward.

Poor Cos often heard the roar of Strong’s choruses and the clinking of glasses from his own room—so near, yet so far. It wasn’t wise to invite Costigan regularly. His drinking was terrible, and when sober, he bored guests with endless stories; when drunk, he dissolved into maudlin tears.

Strange and motley, these were the friends of the Chevalier. Major Pendennis wouldn 辛地欣赏 their company, but Arthur and Warrington liked it well enough—Pen found it as amusing as any high-society gathering he attended.

Each man had a story. They’d all seen luck and misfortune. Most carried schemes in their pockets—plans for rapid wealth. Jack Holt had fought for Don Carlos while Strong fought against him; now he was organizing a tobacco-smuggling operation that promised thirty thousand pounds a year—for just fifteen hundred to bribe the last Excise officer who knew about it.

Tom Diver, former Mexican naval officer, knew of a specie-ship sunk during the war, carrying three hundred eighty thousand dollars and one hundred eighty thousand pounds in bars and doubloons. “Give me eighteen hundred pounds,” he said, “and I’m off tomorrow. Four men, a diving-bell, and I’ll return in ten months to sit in Parliament and buy back my estate!”

Keightley, manager of the underwater Tredyddlum and Polwheedle Copper Mines, sang a fine second and ran a Smyrna Sponge Company and a quicksilver venture—soon to make him rich.

Filby had been everything: corporal of dragoons, field-preacher, missionary-agent, actor at a Greenwich fair-booth—where his father’s attorney found him after the old man died, leaving him a famous property from which he now drew no rent, and whose location nobody quite remembered.

Then there was Sir Francis Clavering, Bart., who enjoyed their society despite lacking strong convivial talents. But he was admired for his wealth and status. He told little stories, sang little songs with affability, and had his own past—prisons, stamped papers, and secrets.

When Altamont returned from Paris, stripped of riches despite his diamond-laden mission, Clavering sent Strong to fetch him, pay his bill, and invite him to stay. Strong soon grew tired of the arrangement.

“This fellow’s no gentleman,” he complained to Clavering. “I don’t like walking with him. He dresses like a nigger on a holiday. Took him to the play the other night—he abused the villain actor, swore at him so loudly the boxes wanted to throw him out. Then came ‘The Brigand’—Wallack dies wounded. Altamont burst into tears, called it a d——d shame, sobbed and cursed. Another row. Everyone laughing. Then he wanted to take off his coat and fight the man who laughed. Who is he? Where does he come from? You must tell me the whole story, Frank. You and he have robbed a church together, that’s my belief. You’d better get it off your mind.”

“Hang him!” Clavering replied, his face darkening. “I wish he was dead.”

Strong thought better of pressing further—at least for now. But he resolved, if need arose, to uncover the secret bond between Altamont and Clavering himself.

CHAPTER XLIV. In Which the Colonel Tells a Tale (Or Two) of His Adventures

The morning after the dinner at Grosvenor Place—where Colonel Altamont had made a grand entrance like a man who’d just won the lottery and lost his mind—he emerged from his bedroom at Shepherd’s Inn, looking as if he’d wrestled with a bear in his sleep and lost. He stomped into Captain Strong’s sitting room, where the Chevalier was already deep in the newspaper, puffing on a cigar like a man who believed smoking was an art form best practiced before breakfast.

Strong was the kind of fellow who could turn any lodging house into a palace. He’d already polished off a breakfast fit for a king—fried eggs, broiled rashers, and all the trimmings—prepared by Mr. Grady, whose culinary skills were so precise they might have been approved by a French chef. Good-natured, talkative, and utterly incapable of being alone, Strong would’ve welcomed even a badger into his home if it meant someone to chat with. Though he didn’t exactly *like* Altamont—indeed, he’d have cheered if the Colonel had suddenly vanished into thin air (a fate Sir Francis Clavering had long desired)—he kept up appearances. They were neighbors, after all, and neighbors don’t usually go around wishing each other dead unless there’s a particularly strong motive.

The night before, Strong had seen Altamont to bed with polite concern, taken away his candle to prevent fire hazards, and discovered that the spirit bottle he’d counted on for a midnight pick-me-up was empty. No matter. He drank a glass of water instead—because, really, what’s a little thirst compared to dignity?—and went to sleep like a man who’d never known stress. He woke refreshed, rosy-cheeked, and ready to face whatever the day threw at him: war, prison, or a particularly awkward conversation with a colonel who looked suspiciously like a man who’d dyed his whiskers and was hiding something under his wig.

Altamont’s first act upon entering the room was to bellow at Grady for a pint of pale ale. The servant delivered it promptly. The Colonel poured it into a pewter flagon, then into his mouth, drained it, set the tankard down, took a deep breath, wiped his mouth with his dressing gown (which, incidentally, contrasted sharply with his dyed whiskers—something Strong had noticed but wisely chosen not to comment on), and declared himself “immensely refreshed.”

“Nothing like that beer,” he announced, “when the coppers are hot.” He paused. “Many a day I’ve drunk a dozen of Bass at Calcutta, and—and——”

“And at Lucknow, I suppose?” Strong said with a grin. “I got the beer for you on purpose. Knew you’d need it after last night.”

Altamont launched into a tale of woe and whiskey.

“I cannot help myself,” he said, beating his head with a hand the size of a ham. “I’m a madman when I get the liquor on board me. Ain’t fit to be trusted with a spirit-bottle. Once I start, I can’t stop until it’s gone. And once it’s gone, Lord knows what I say or don’t say.”

He sighed. “I dined quietly here last night. Grady gave me two tumblers—just two—and I intended to spend the evening at the Black and Red as sober as a parson. But why did you leave that confounded sample-bottle of Hollands out, Strong? Grady had to go out, and left the kettle boiling for tea. It was no use. I couldn’t resist. Washed it all down, sir, by Jove. And I suspect I had more later at that infernal little thieves’ den.”

“What, were you there too?” Strong asked. “Before you came to Grosvenor Place? That was early.”

“Early hours to be drunk and cleared out before nine o’clock, eh? But so it was. Yes, like a great big fool, I must go there. Found the fellows dining—Blackland, young Moss, and half a dozen other rogues. If we’d gone to Rouge et Noir, I must have won. But we didn’t try the black and red. No, hang ’em—they knew I’d beat ’em. So they played hazard on the dining table instead. And I dropped all the money you gave me this morning. Be hanged to my luck. That’s what set me wild. I remember thinking I’d go get more from Clavering. Then… well, then I don’t remember much until I woke this morning and heard old Bows at No. 4 playing the piano.”

Strong lit his cigar with a coal and mused. “I should like to know how you always draw money from Clavering, Colonel.”

Altamont burst out laughing. “Ha, ha! He owes it to me.”

“I don’t know that that’s a reason with Frank,” Strong replied. “He owes plenty besides you.”

“Well, he gives it to me because he loves me like a brother,” the Colonel said, grinning like a man who’d just stolen a pie and was pretending it was a gift. “You know he does, Captain.—No?—He don’t?—Well, perhaps he don’t. And if you ask me no questions, perhaps I’ll tell you no lies, Captain Strong—put that in your pipe and smoke it, my boy.”

“But I’ll give up that confounded brandy-bottle,” Altamont continued, after a pause. “Must give it up, or it’ll be the ruin of me.”

“It makes you say queer things,” said Strong, eyeing him carefully. “Remember what you said last night at Clavering’s table.”

“Say? What did I say?” Altamont asked, suddenly alarmed. “Did I split anything? Dammy, Strong, did I split anything?”

“Ask me no questions, and I will tell you no lies,” Strong replied, quoting back the phrase with a smirk. He thought of the words Altamont had muttered—words that had sent him fleeing from Clavering’s house the moment he saw Major Pendennis. But Strong decided not to press the issue. “No,” he said finally, “you didn’t split, Colonel. It was only a trap of mine to see if I could make you speak. But you didn’t say a word anyone could understand—you were too far gone for that.”

So much the better, thought Altamont, and heaved a great sigh of relief. Strong noticed, but said nothing. The Colonel, now in full storytelling mode, pressed on.

“Yes, I own to my faults,” he said. “There are three things I can’t resist, no matter what: a bottle of brandy, a box of dice, and a beautiful woman. No man of pluck and spirit ever could, as I know of. There’s hardly a country in the world where those three haven’t gotten me into trouble.”

“Indeed?” said Strong.

“From the age of fifteen, when I ran away from home and became a cabin-boy on an Indiaman, till now, when I’m nearly fifty, women have always been my downfall. Why, one of them—black eyes, jewels, battens and ermine like a duchess—she swept off the best part of a thousand pounds I’d brought with me.”

He leaned forward. “Didn’t I ever tell you about it? Well, I don’t mind. At first I was cautious. Had a lot of money, kept it close, lived like a gentleman—Colonel Altamont, Meurice’s hotel, the whole shebang. Never played except at public tables, and won more than I lost. Then I met a chap at the hotel—a swell, white kid gloves, tuft on his chin—Bloundell-Bloundell, his name was. Asked me to dinner, took me to Madame the Countess de Foljambe’s soirées. Such a woman, Strong! Such an eye! Such a hand at the piano! Lor bless you, she’d sit down and sing, gaze at you, until she warbled your soul right out of your body.”

He paused. “She invited me to her parties every Tuesday. I took opera boxes, gave her dinners at the restaurateur’s—what else? But it wasn’t the dinners or the opera boxes that cost Clavering’s money. No, be hanged to it—it was swept off another way.”

One night at the Countess’s, several of them were at supper—Mr. Bloundell-Bloundell, the Honourable Deuceace, the Marky de la Tour de Force—all top-tier nobility, the height of fashion. Champagne flowed, then brandy. The Countess mixed punch for him. Cards followed. He played and drank until he didn’t know what he was doing. “I was like I was last night,” he said. “I was taken away and put to bed somehow. Woke the next day with a roaring headache, and my servant told me the Honourable Deuceace wanted to see me.”

“‘How are you, Colonel?’ he said, stepping into my bedroom. ‘How long did you stay after I left? The game was getting too high for me—I’d lost enough to you for one night.’”

“‘To me?’ I said. ‘How’s that, my dear feller?’—though he was an Earl’s son, we were as familiar as you and me. ‘How’s that?’ I said, and he told me he’d borrowed thirty louis at vingt-et-un, gave me an I.O.U., which I tucked into my pocket-book before he left.”

I pulled it out—Countess had worked it for me—and sure enough, there it was. He paid me thirty louis in gold right there at my bedside. I said he was a gentleman, offered him a drink—no, he doesn’t drink in the morning—and he left.

Then came another knock. This time it was Bloundell-Bloundell and the Marky.

“‘Bong jour, Marky,’ I said. ‘Good morning—no headache?’”

“‘No,’ he said. ‘But Deuceace has been here. We met him in the Palais Royal. Has he settled with you? Get it while you can—he’s slippery. And since he won three ponies from Bloundell, I recommend you collect your money while he still has some.’”

“‘He’s paid me,’ I said. ‘But I didn’t know he owed me anything. I don’t remember lending him thirty louis.’”

They exchanged glances. Bloundell said, “Colonel, you’re a queer feller. No man could’ve guessed you’d had more than tea all night. Yet you forget everything in the morning. Come, come—tell that to the marines.”

“‘En efet,’ said the Marky, twirling his little black mustache in the mirror. ‘Let us speak of affairs. Colonel, you understand that matters of honour are best settled at once. Perhaps it won’t inconvenience you to settle our little matters from last night.’”

“‘What little matters?’ I asked. ‘Do you owe me money, Marky?’”

“‘Bah!’ he said. ‘No more jesting. I have your note of hand for three hundred and forty louis. La voia!’ And he pulled it out.”

“‘And mine for two hundred and ten,’ said Bloundell, pulling out his own.”

I leapt out of bed, wrapped my dressing gown around me. “Are you here to make a fool of me? I don’t owe you two hundred, or two thousand, or two louis. I won’t pay a farthing. Do you think I’ll be caught with your notes of hand? I laugh at them—and at you. I believe you’re a couple——”

“‘A couple of what?’ asked Bloundell. ‘You’re aware, Colonel Altamont, that we are men of honour. We’re not here to trifle or listen to abuse. You will either pay us—or we will expose you as a cheat, and chastise you as a cheat, too.’”

“‘Oui, parbleu,’ said the Marky—but I didn’t care. I could’ve thrown him out the window. But Blound ell—big man, three stone heavier, six inches taller—he could’ve done for me.”

“‘Monsieur will pay, or Monsieur will give me the reason why. I believe you’re little better than a polisson, Colonel Altamont.’”

That was the phrase he used. Altamont grinned. “I got plenty more of that language. I was in the thick of it when another friend arrived—one I’d met at Boulogne, hadn’t played the night before, actually warned me against Bloundell and the others. I told him the story. He said: ‘I’m very sorry. You insisted on large stakes. The Countess begged you to stop. These gentlemen offered repeatedly to quit. It was you who pushed.’”

He charged me dead. When they left, he told me the Marky would shoot me for sure. “She hates these two men,” he said. “She’s warned you repeatedly against them.” And then—this is the kicker—he said: “It’s my belief that that woman is distractedly in love with you.”

“Do you think so?” I asked.

And he told me she’d actually knelt before him and said, “Save Colonel Altamont!”

As soon as I was dressed, I went to see her. She shrieked and nearly fainted. Called me Ferdinand. I’m blest if she didn’t.

“I thought your name was Jack,” Strong said, laughing.

“A man may have more names than one, mayn’t he?” Altamont asked, blushing behind his dyed whiskers. “When I’m with a lady, I like to take a good one. She called me by my Christian name. She cried fit to break your heart. I can’t stand seeing a woman cry—not when I’m fond of her. She said she couldn’t bear the thought of my losing so much money in her house. Wouldn’t I take her diamonds and necklaces and pay part?”

I swore I wouldn’t touch a farthing’s worth. Maybe I didn’t think they were worth much—but what can a woman do more than give you her all? That’s the sort I like. And I told her not to worry—the money was nothing. I wouldn’t pay a single farthing.

“Then they’ll shoot you,” she said. “They’ll kill my Ferdinand.”

“They’ll kill my Jack wouldn’t have sounded well in French,” Strong said, laughing.

“Never mind names,” said Altamont, sulkily. “A man of honour may take any name he chooses, I suppose.”

“Go on,” said Strong.

“She said they’d kill me.”

“No,” I said. “They won’t. I won’t let that scamp of a Marquis send me out of the world. If he lays a hand on me, I’ll brain him, Marquis as he is.”

At this, the Countess shrank back. “Do I understand Colonel Altamont aright?” she said. “That a British officer refuses to meet any person who provokes him to the field of honour?”

“Field of honour be hanged, Countess,” I said. “You wouldn’t have me be a target for that little scoundrel’s pistol practice.”

“Colonel Altamont,” she said, “I thought you were a man of honour—I thought, I—but no matter. Good-bye, sir.”

And she swept out, voice choking in her handkerchief.

“Countess!” I shouted, rushing after her, seizing her hand.

“Leave me, Monsieur le Colonel,” she said, shaking me off. “My father was a General of the Grand Army. A soldier should know how to pay all his debts of honour.”

What could I do? Everyone was against me. Caroline said I’d lost the money—though I didn’t remember a thing. I’d taken Deuceace’s money too—but he’d offered it, so that was different. Every one of these chaps was a man of fashion and honour. The Marky and the Countess were of the first families in France. And, by Jove, rather than offend her, I paid the money—five hundred and sixty gold Napoleons—plus three hundred I lost when I had my revenge.

And I can’t tell you at this minute whether I was done or not,” concluded Altamont, musing. “Sometimes I think I was. But then Caroline was so fond of me. That woman would never have seen me done. Never. I’m sure she wouldn’t. At least, if she would, I’m deceived in woman.”

Just as Altamont was about to dive into further revelations, a knock echoed through the chamber. Grady opened the door—and in walked none other than Sir Francis Clavering.

“The Governor, by Jove,” Strong said, surprised. “What’s brought you here?”

“Not good, I warrant,” growled Altamont, glaring at the Baronet under his heavy eyebrows. And indeed, good rarely brought Sir Francis Clavering anywhere.

Whenever he appeared at Shepherd’s Inn, it was money that lured him. There was usually a money-lender waiting in Strong’s chambers—or Campion’s below—with bills to negotiate or renew. Clavering was a man who’d never faced his debts honestly. As long as he could renew a bill, his conscience stayed clear. He’d sign almost anything for tomorrow, so long as today remained untouched. Fortune couldn’t save him permanently—he was built to be ruined, to cheat small tradesmen, to be duped by sharper rogues, to be niggardly and reckless, as dishonest as those who cheated him. He was a dupe because he was too mean to be a successful villain. He’d told more lies, endured more shameful schemes to avoid a debt, than enough to fund a fortune for a braver rogue. Even at his peak, he was abject and shuffling. Had he been a Crown Prince, he couldn’t have been weaker, more useless, more dissolute, or more ungrateful. He couldn’t move through life without leaning on someone else—and yet he distrusted every agent he hired, sabotaged plans meant to help him, and secretly undermined those he employed.

Strong knew Clavering. He judged him correctly. They weren’t friends. The Chevalier worked for his patron—as he might have marched through a siege or endured hardship in battle—because it was his duty, because he’d agreed to it. “What does he want?” the officers of the Shepherd’s Inn garrison wondered whenever the Baronet arrived.

Clavering’s face was pale, angry. “So, sir,” he said, addressing Altamont, “you’ve been at your old tricks.”

“Which ones?” asked Altamont, sneering.

“You’ve been at the Rouge et Noir. You were there last night,” Clavering screamed.

“How do you know?” Altamont asked. “Were you there? I was at the Club—but not on the colours. Ask the Captain—I’ve been telling him about it. It was with the bones. Hazard, Sir Francis, upon my word and honour it was.”

He looked at Clavering with mock humility so knowing it only made the Baronet angrier.

“What the deuce do I care, sir, how a man like you loses his money—whether it’s hazard or roulette?” Clavering yelled, spewing oaths. “What I will not have is that you use my name, or couple it with yours. Damn him, Strong, why don’t you keep him in better order? He’s drawn a bill on me, lost the money at the table—I can’t stand it! Flesh and blood won’t bear it. Do you know how much I’ve paid for you, sir?”

“This was only a little’un, Sir Francis—only fifteen pounds, Captain Strong. They wouldn’t stand another. And it oughtn’t to anger you, Governor. Why, it’s so trifling I didn’t even mention it to Strong—did I now, Captain? I protest it had quite slipped my memory, all on account of that confounded liquor I took.”

“Liquor or no liquor, sir, it’s no business of mine. I don’t care what you drink, or where you drink it—only it shan’t be in my house. And I will not have you breaking into my house of a night, a fellow like you intruding on my company. How dared you show yourself in Grosvenor Place last night, sir—and what do you suppose my friends must think of me when they see a man of your sort walking into my dining-room uninvited, drunk, calling for liquor as if you were the master of the house?”

“They’ll think you know some very queer sort of people, I dare say,” Altamont said, impenetrably cheerful. “Look here, Baronet, I apologise. On my honour I do. And ain’t an apology enough between two gentlemen? It was a strong measure, walking into your cuddy and calling for drink as if I was the Captain. But I’d had too much before—that’s why I wanted more. Nothing could be simpler. And it was because they wouldn’t give me more money on your name at the Black and Red that I thought I’d come down and speak to you about it. To refuse me was nothing. But to refuse a bill drawn on you—after all you’ve done for the shop, and being a baronet, member of parliament, gentleman and no mistake—Damme, it’s ungrateful.”

“By heavens, if ever you do it again—if ever you dare show yourself in my house, or give my name at a gambling-house or any other house, by Jove—or give any reference to me, or speak to me in the street, by God, or anywhere else until I speak to you—I disclaim you altogether. I won’t give you another shilling.”

“Governor, don’t be provoking,” Altamont said surlily. “Don’t talk to me about daring to do this or that. Or when my dander’s up, it’s the very thing to urge me on. I oughtn’t to have come last night—I know I oughtn’t. But I told you I was drunk. And that ought to be sufficient between gentleman and gentleman.”

“You a gentleman! Dammy, sir,” said the Baronet, “how dares a fellow like you call himself a gentleman?”

Every day after the grand social events at Grosvenor Place and Greenwich—where Major Pendennis had been seen enjoying himself like a man who’d just discovered that champagne is not, in fact, a beverage reserved only for kings—the old gentleman’s affection for the Clavering family seemed to grow stronger than ever. His visits became so frequent they might as well have been scheduled by the household staff. He brought gifts, offered advice, and generally behaved as though he were the family’s unofficial but very enthusiastic uncle.

He was, after all, an old man about town—a seasoned veteran of society’s glittering trenches—and had managed to get invited to places where even Lady Clavering ought to be seen. “Would your ladyship care to attend the grand entertainment at Gaunt House?” he’d ask with the air of someone delivering divine prophecy. “Or perhaps you’d prefer the breakfast ball at Viscount Marrowfat’s? It’s said to be *very* pretty. And there’ll be a Watteau quadrille—Miss Amory would look simply divine in it!”

He was always ready to escort her, or to assist Sir Francis in any way agreeable to the latter—which, given the Baronet’s current standing, probably meant helping him avoid arrest.

Now, despite his noble lineage and decent income (though we’re not sure how decent), Clavering remained something of a pariah in polite circles. Clubs blackballed him twice. In Parliament, he conversed mostly with members whose reputations were already tarnished beyond repair—men who could only be described as “the kind of people you don’t want to meet in a dark alley.” Among them: Captain Raff, the honourable member for Epsom, who retired after the Goodwood races to accept a mission to the Levant (which, according to Mr. Hotspur, the whip of the party, was code for “being sent somewhere no one else wanted to go”); Hustingson, patriotic MP for Islington, now Governor of Coventry Island (a post which conveniently silenced his denunciations of corruption); and Bob Freeny, of the Booterstown Freenys, who was famously a dead shot—so much so that the author feels compelled to say, “We therefore wish to speak with every respect.”

And yet, among all these gentlemen, none did Mr. Hotspur despise more than Sir Francis Clavering. “If that man is needed for a division,” Hotspur declared, “ten to one he’ll be found in a hell. Educated in the Fleet, never heard the end of Newgate yet. Will muddle away the Begum’s fortune at thimble-rig, get caught picking pockets, and end up on board the hulks.”

And if Hotspur, a man of high birth and impeccable judgment, could still maintain a civil façade toward Clavering for professional reasons—why couldn’t Major Pendennis do the same?

“Because,” said the Major, “he has a very good cellar and a very good cook. As long as he keeps quiet, he’s not offensive. And he rarely speaks. If he chooses to lose his money at gambling tables to blacklegs, what’s it to me? Don’t pry into people’s affairs, Pen, my boy. Every man has a cupboard he doesn’t want you to open. Why bother peering inside when the rest of the house is wide open? And what a house it is! The women are excellent. The Begum may not be refined—she calls ‘Apollo’ ‘Apoller’—but she’s kinder than most and cleverer than most too. And as for little Blanche… well, I’ve told you my opinion. She’s sweet on you, you rogue. You know she’d marry you if you asked. But you’re growing such a great man now, I suppose you won’t settle for a Duke’s daughter—eh, sir? I recommend you try one. Ask one. Try.”

Pen, perhaps slightly inflated by his recent success in life, allowed this idea to float around his mind like a balloon tethered to a garden gate. His uncle’s constant hints didn’t hurt either. Wasn’t Miss Amory, after all, showing signs of interest in renewing their early flirtation—one that had once bloomed beside the tranquil banks of the rural Brawl? Still, Pen professed no desire for marriage. “I’m happy alone,” he said, adopting his uncle’s worldly tone. “With a wife, I’d lose my place in society. I don’t fancy retiring to the country with Mrs. Pendennis, nor taking her into lodgings to be waited on by the servant-of-all-work. My illusions are over. You cured me of my first love—foolish girl, and she’d have made a fool of a husband, and a very sulky one at that. We young fellows live fast. At twenty-five, I feel as ancient as those old bachelors in the bow-window at Bays’s. Don’t be offended—I just mean I’m bored with romance. I couldn’t stir myself to fall in love with Miss Amory again any more than I could adore Lady Mirabel all over again. I wish I could. I rather like old Mirabel for being so infatuated—he’s got the most respectable part of his life in his passion.”

“Sir Charles Mirabel was always theatrical,” the Major snapped, annoyed that his nephew should speak flippantly of someone of such rank. “He acted at Carlton House when he was Page to the Prince. He’s always been mixed up with that sort of thing. He could afford to marry whom he liked. And Lady Mirabel is a most respectable woman—received everywhere, mind you. The Duchess of Connaught receives her. Lady Rockminster receives her. Young men shouldn’t speak lightly of people in that station. There’s not a more respectable woman in England than Lady Mirabel. And those old fogies at Bays’s? They’re some of the first gentlemen in England. You youngsters should learn manners, breeding, modesty.”

The Major began to suspect Pen was becoming overly pert, too full of himself, and far too popular for his own good.

Pen found this amusing. He studied his uncle’s quirks with the delight of a scholar examining rare fossils. “I’ve been a youngster for fifteen years now,” he said smoothly. “And if you think we’re disrespectful, you should see the present generation. A protege of yours came to breakfast with me the other day. You told me to invite him, so I did. We saw the sights, dined at the club, went to the play. He said the wine at the Polyanthus wasn’t as good as Ellis’s at Richmond, smoked Warrington’s cavendish after breakfast, and when I gave him a sovereign as a farewell gift, said he had plenty, but would take it to show he wasn’t proud.”

“Did he?” the Major exclaimed, instantly appeased. “You asked young Clavering? Fine boy—rather wild, but a fine boy. Parents like that sort of attention. Can’t do better than pay it to our worthy friends at Grosvenor Place. Took him to the play and tipped him? That was right, sir—that was right!” With that, the Mentor abandoned Telemachus, deciding the youth weren’t so bad after all—and that he might yet make something of that fellow.

As Master Clavering grew taller and stronger, he became less inclined to obey his fond parents and governess. Instead, he ruled the household. With his father, he was silent and sullen, avoiding him entirely. With his mother, he roared and fought whenever she refused him anything—especially food or sweets. With his governess, he kicked her shins so hard she eventually surrendered, convinced she’d lost the battle before it began. He once tried to bully his sister Blanche too—but she, possessing immense spirit, boxed his ears so soundly that he never attempted it again. She also beat him in arguments, which was impressive considering he hadn’t yet learned to read.

Eventually, Sir Francis decided enough was enough. “The little beggar should be sent to school,” he declared.

So young Frank Clavering was dispatched to the Rev. Otto Rose’s establishment in Twickenham—where future noblemen were prepped for public schools with all the dignity and luxury one might expect from a boarding school run by a man who thought moral discipline was superior to corporal punishment. Which, frankly, was a relief for everyone involved.

At Rose’s, the boys wore varnished boots, cambric handkerchiefs, and lemon-coloured kid gloves so tiny they looked like they belonged to a doll. They dressed in shawl dressing-gowns for dinner, had fires in their bedrooms, horse-and-carriage exercise occasionally, and oil for their hair. No flogging. Just enlightenment. And progress.

They progressed rapidly—so rapidly, in fact, that by age ten, young Frank had mastered the art of drinking spirits and smoking cigars. He stole his father’s Havannahs and smoked them in the stables. He drank champagne with the confidence of a seasoned officer. At ten years old, he was already a man among men—except that he still wet the bed sometimes.

When he returned home for holidays, Major Pendennis treated him with the same laborious civility he showed the rest of the family—even though the boy mocked him behind his back, calling him “Old Wigsby” and drawing caricatures of him with exaggerated noses and wigs that looked like they’d been blown off a shipwreck.

But the Major was undeterred. He encouraged Arthur to invite the boy to his chambers, to dine at the club, to visit Madame Tussaud’s, the Tower, the theatre—and to tip him at the end of each day. Arthur, being kind-hearted and fond of children, obliged. He took the boy to breakfast at the Temple, where the lad made contemptuous remarks about the furniture, the crockery, and the tattered state of Warrington’s dressing-gown. Then he smoked a short pipe and recounted a fight between Tuffy and Long Biggings at school, greatly edifying the two hosts.

Lady Clavering was grateful—more so than the boy, who took everything as his due and likely had more sovereigns in his pocket than poor Pen, who generously gave him one from his own meagre supply.

The Major, with the sharp eyes of experience and the glasses of age, watched the boy closely. He didn’t pry, but he observed. As a neighbour, a friend, and a man of the world, he took it upon himself to learn about Lady Clavering’s finances, her capital, and what the children stood to inherit.

And soon, he had a clear picture. The daughter would get little. The son—well, the son would get everything. Sir Francis didn’t care for anyone. His wife adored him. His daughter disliked him. Simple as that.

“Ah, my dear madam,” the Major said, patting the boy’s head, “this boy may wear a baron’s coronet one day—if things are managed rightly. If Sir Francis would only play his cards well.”

Lady Clavering sighed deeply. “He plays too many of his cards already, Major. I’m afraid.”

The Major admitted he knew. He’d heard of Clavering’s unfortunate habit of gambling. He pitied her sincerely. But he spoke with such genuine sympathy that she confided in him—about her first marriage, her late husband, her regrets.

She told him how, as a child sent back to India after two years in Europe, she’d met Amory and foolishly married him. “Oh, you don’t know how miserable he made me,” she said. “Between him and my father, I lived in hell. Before I met him, I’d never seen a man except my father’s clerks and servants. We didn’t go into society in India—because of…” (“I know,” said the Major, bowing.) “I was a wild romantic child. My head was full of novels. He talked beautifully of calm nights at sea, and I thought he was the hero of my dreams. So I married him. And from that day, I was wretched. My father wasn’t a good man—neither to my mother nor to me. Amory was worse. A spendthrift, a drunkard, furious when drunk. Not a faithful husband. If he’d died in jail before the trial instead of after, he’d have saved me a lot of shame.”

“And my second husband?” she added. “I’m not happy in him. I’m not happy in my daughter. She’s not a good girl—though you wouldn’t know it. She’s cost me many tears. She sneers at me because I lack learning. How could I? I was raised among natives until twelve, then sent back to India at fourteen. Ah, Major, I should have been a good woman if I’d had a good husband. Now I must go upstairs and wipe my eyes—they’re red from crying. Lady Rockminster is coming, and we’re going for a drive in the Park.”

And when Lady Rockminster arrived, not a trace of tears remained. Lady Clavering bounced out with her usual blunders and liveliness, murdering the English language with gusto.

“Begad,” the Major thought to himself, “she’s not such a bad woman. Not refined, certainly—calls ‘Apollo’ ‘Apoller’—but she’s got heart. And a devilish amount of money. Three stars in India Stock. That young cub’s getting it all—is he?”

He wished a little of it might go to Miss Blanche. Better still, one of those stars in the name of Mr. Arthur Pendennis.

Still pursuing his schemes—whatever they might be—the old diplomat began talking to Miss Blanche in a kindly, fatherly way whenever he found her alone. He visited so often, became so familiar with the ladies, that they didn’t hesitate to argue in front of him. Lady Clavering, loud-tongued and quick-tempered, frequently quarrelled with her daughter in his presence.

Blanche’s wit usually won. Her arrows pierced her mother’s defenses, driving her away in defeat.
