“I’m an old fellow,” the Major said. “I’ve nothing to do. I keep good counsel. I’m the friend of both of you. If you choose to quarrel before me, I won’t tell anyone. But you’re both good people. I intend to make peace between you. I’ve done it for others—husbands and wives, fathers and sons, daughters and mothers. I like it. I’ve nothing else to do.”

One day, he entered the drawing-room just as Lady Clavering stormed out, clearly furious. “She couldn’t speak to him now,” she muttered. “Too angry with that—that—little, wicked—” Anger choked the rest.

“My dear Miss Amory,” the Major said gently, entering. “I see what’s happened. You and your mother have disagreed. Mothers and daughters disagree even in the best families. Last week, I reconciled Lady Clapperton and her daughter Lady Claudia. Lady Lear hasn’t spoken to her eldest daughter in fourteen years. Kinder, more worthy people I’ve never known—except to each other. But they can’t live together. They shouldn’t. And I wish, my dear creature, with all my soul, that I could see you with your own establishment—no woman in London could manage one better. Your own home, your own happiness.”

“I’m not very happy here,” said the Sylphide. “And my mother’s stupidity is enough to provoke a saint.”

“Precisely,” said the Major. “You’re not suited. Your mother made one mistake—was it nature, my dear, in your case?—she shouldn’t have educated you. You were raised to be brilliant, refined, surrounded by fools. Your place is to lead, not follow. I’ve watched you, Miss Amory. You’re ambitious. Your sphere is command. You can’t shine here. I hope I’ll see you in another, happier home—mistress of it.”

Blanche shrugged. “Where is the Prince? Where is the palace, Major Pendennis? I’m ready. But there’s no romance left. No real affection.”

“No indeed,” said the Major, with a sentimental sigh.

“I don’t know anything about it,” Blanche confessed, looking down. “Only what I’ve read in novels.”

“Of course not,” said the Major. “How could you? And novels aren’t true. There’s no romance left in the world. Begad, I wish I were a young fellow like my nephew.”

“And what,” continued Miss Amory, musing, “what are the men we see at balls—dancing guardsmen, penniless clerks, bores? If I had my brother’s fortune, I might have the establishment you promise. But with my name and my little means—what am I to look to? A country parson? A barrister near Russell Square? A captain in dragoons who takes lodgings and comes home smelling of smoke like Sir Francis Clavering? That’s how we girls are destined to end life. O Major Pendennis, I’m sick of London, of balls, of young dandies with chin-tips, of great ladies who know us one day and cut us the next. I’d like to leave it all and go into a convent. I shall never find anyone who understands me. I live here as alone as if I were locked in a cell forever. I wish there were Sisters of Charity here. I’d join them, catch the plague, and die of it. I wish to quit the world. I’m not very old—but I’m tired. I’ve suffered. I’m disillusioned. I’m weary. O that the Angel of Death would come and beckon me away!”

Which, of course, was just a dramatic way of saying: *Lady Flamingo cut me last night. I couldn’t get an invitation to Lady Drum’s ball. Nobody’s asking me to dance. Dora, Flora, Leonora—all engaged. Nobody wants me.*

“You judge wisely,” the Major said kindly. “The Prince doesn’t marry nowadays unless the Princess has a doosid deal of money—or is of equal rank. The young ones marry within their class. If no fortune, they have each other’s shoulders to push forward. A girl with your fortune can’t expect a great match. But a girl with your genius, tact, and manners—*with a clever husband*—can make her mark anywhere. We’re grown doosid republican. Talent ranks with birth and wealth now, begad. A clever man with a clever wife can take any place they please.”

Ah, the noble Henry Foker—our gallant, melancholy, and altogether too romantic gentleman—has been busy these past few pages doing what any man of his temperament would do when love is in the air: chasing it like a hare with a broken leg, and failing to catch it every time.

I’d advise my younger readers—especially those who’ve recently discovered the joy of sighing at dinner tables or writing poetry on napkins—to pause for a moment and consider how much of their lives they’re spending on this grand passion. It’s not just time lost, you know—it’s *everything* lost. Sleep? Gone. Breakfast? Served cold while you stare into space, wondering if she’ll wear blue tonight. Work? Forgotten, like a forgotten hat left on a park bench. Friends? Left behind, as you rush off to see if her carriage has passed by the Serpentine. Even your own family becomes a nuisance—your mother’s stories about the weather seem unbearable; your father’s jokes fall flat. You barely taste the soup, and yet you’re still hungry—for her.

And so poor Mr. H. Foker, bred to indulge every whim (which he did with the enthusiasm of a man who believes that self-indulgence is a virtue), found himself utterly captivated by Miss Blanche Amory. He had once given Arthur Pendennis some sound advice—about life, love, and the dangers of marrying a woman with a bad temper. But now, dear reader, even the wisest man can be undone by a pretty face and a well-timed glance. Who among us hasn’t preached against passion—and then fallen headfirst into it? I say again: we all have. We preach to others, but practice on ourselves. And if you’re one of those rare souls who never felt anything stronger than mild curiosity, then I say: good for you! But also—boring. We don’t need angels in this story. We need people who stumble, who blunder, who cry over lost gloves and laugh at the wrong moments. We want the real ones—the ones who get drunk, fall in love, and occasionally forget their own names.

So let’s not pretend that only perfect beings are worthy of admiration. The world is full of odd couples: the transported pickpocket who fell madly in love with a female convict—both ancient, unattractive, and fond of gin—but whose romance was no less passionate than Antony and Cleopatra’s. Or the Polish dwarf, Count Borulawski, who made the most beautiful baroness at Dresden swoon with devotion. And then there’s young Cornet Tozer, who ran off with Mrs. Battersby—old enough to be his grandmother, mind you—and declared it the happiest day of his life.

So yes, Henry Foker was smitten. Deeply, desperately, absurdly smitten. When Lord Gravesend’s family decamped to the country (leaving behind a proxy for the venerable Lord Bagwig), Harry stayed behind in London—not because anyone missed him, especially Lady Ann, whom he was supposed to marry. She didn’t miss him. Not even a little. But Harry? Oh, he was following Miss Clavering like a dog after a squirrel. And since everyone knew he was engaged to Lady Ann, he couldn’t exactly announce his new affection. So he kept it locked up inside—like a volcano waiting to erupt. One day, he might just burst open from the pressure of his own feelings and die a noble death of emotional overload.

But let’s skip ahead to a certain June evening, when Gaunt House hosted a ball so grand it could have been mistaken for a royal coronation. The next day’s newspapers printed two whole columns of guests—names so numerous and aristocratic they could have filled a telephone directory. Among them were Sir Francis and Lady Clavering, Miss Amory (courtesy of Major Pendennis’s tireless efforts), and our two heroes: Arthur and Harry.

They danced. A lot. Especially with Miss Blanche. The Major, meanwhile, took charge of Lady Clavering—his duty being to escort her to the refreshment room, where the feast was more magnificent than a king’s banquet. There, amid portraits of Titian and Giorgione, gold salvers, wax candles shaped like stars, and mountains of food, Lady Clavering ate with the determination of a bear preparing for winter. The Major followed her like a loyal shadow, fetching plates, adjusting chairs, calling servants, and enduring the entire ordeal with a smile that said, “I am suffering, but I am dignified.”

Mr. Wagg, ever the statistician, counted the dishes she consumed—though, having imbibed a generous amount of champagne, his tally may have included a few phantom peaches and invisible soups. He advised Lady Steyne’s doctor, Mr. Honeyman, to check on the Begum the next morning—just in case.

Sir Francis, however, didn’t last long. After a couple of glasses of wine, he slipped away to Jermyn Street, where his friends—Loder, Punter, Moss Abrams, and Captain Skewball—were gathered around the green table, playing cards and drinking themselves into a state of cheerful idiocy. Sir Francis rejoined them, spirits lifted by the familiar rattle of dice and the sound of men laughing at nothing.

Now, during the dancing, Mr. Pynsent—a man whose name sounds like a tax form—asked Miss Amory to dance. But when he saw Arthur Pendennis nearby, he scowled. Arthur, sensing danger, sprang up and claimed Miss Amory as his partner. Pynsent bit his lip, bowed deeply, and retreated, muttering something about surrendering his claim.

“Who *is* that man?” thought Arthur. “A provincial fool with a novel under his arm. His soul is in Downing Street. His neckcloth is foolscap. His hair is sand. His legs are rulers. His insides are tape and sealing-wax. He was a prig in his cradle and has never laughed since birth—except three times, at the same joke told by his boss. I hate him. I hate him as much as I hate cold boiled veal.”

Blanche, naturally, responded with, “Oh, Mr. Pendennis, you’re wicked, mechant, perfectly abominable!”—but secretly thrilled at the attention.

“I say,” Arthur replied, “you have the most beautiful figure and the slimmest waist in the world—Miss Amory, I mean. I beg your pardon. Another turn? This music would make an alderman dance.”

“And you’ve stopped tumbling when you waltz now?” Blanche asked, looking up at him with a mischievous glint.

“One falls and gets up again in life, Blanche,” Arthur said. “You know I used to call you that. It’s the prettiest name in the world. Besides, I’ve practiced.”

“And with many partners, I’m afraid,” she said, shrugging dramatically.

Well, yes. Arthur had practiced. He’d become quite the dancer.

Meanwhile, poor Harry Foker—usually so chatty and cheerful—was utterly silent when dancing with Blanche. Holding her waist was ecstasy. Whirling around the room with her was divine. But speaking? What could he possibly say? What clever remark could match her wit? What pearl of wisdom could be worthy of such a queen?

It was Blanche who did all the talking. She asked about his little pony—how it was doing, whether it was still sweet. She thanked him tenderly, then sighed delicately when he offered to give it to her. “I have nobody to ride with in London,” she said. “Mamma is timid, and her figure isn’t pretty on horseback. Sir Francis never goes out with me. He loves me like—like a stepdaughter. Oh, how delightful it must be to have a father—a father, Mr. Foker!”

“Oh, uncommon,” Harry said, completely missing the sentimental mood.

At which point, Blanche’s eyes sparkled with laughter, and Harry—now relaxed—began telling her silly stories about horses, himself, and various people in the room. He spoke with the charm of a man who thinks he’s funny, though most of his jokes were probably about stable boys and bad weather.

When the conversation lagged, Blanche revived it by asking about Logwood. Was it pretty? Did he hunt? Did he like women who hunted? (She was ready to declare herself a hunting enthusiast.)

Harry, however, disapproved of sporting ladies. He pointed out Lady Bullfinch, passing by with a cigar in her mouth, as proof. Blanche agreed—she hated the idea of foxes being killed. “It would make me shudder,” she said.

Harry laughed and waltzed with renewed vigor.

At the end of the night, as they danced their final waltz, Blanche asked about Drummington. Was it a fine house? Her cousins were very accomplished—had she met Lord Erith? Which cousin did he favor? Wasn’t it Lady Ann? Yes, she was sure it was. She could tell by his blushes.

“I’m tired,” she said. “It’s late. I must go to mamma.” And without another word, she slipped from Harry’s arm and grabbed Arthur’s. “Mamma, mamma! Take me to mamma, dear Mr. Pendennis!” she called, shooting Harry a parting glance that could have melted steel.

My Lord Steyne arrived, resplendent in garter and ribbon, bald head gleaming, whiskers red as fire. He made a grand impression on Lady Clavering. With his own hand, he handed her a glass of wine, complimented her daughter, and asked to be introduced to her. At that moment, Arthur appeared with Blanche.

The peer bowed low. Blanche curtsied deeper than any courtier had ever done. He shook Arthur’s hand, praised his book—“very wicked and clever”—and asked Blanche if she’d read it. Arthur winced. Because Blanche was one of the heroines—Neaera in *Walter Lorraine*, slightly altered, with black ringlets instead of golden ones.

She had read it. Her eyes said everything: admiration, delight, recognition.

Lord Steyne bowed twice more and moved on.

As soon as he was gone, mother and daughter whispered praises. “He said they make a very nice couple,” murmured the Major to Lady Clavering. “Did he now? Really?” Mamma was thrilled. She tapped Arthur on the arm, tapped Blanche, tapped the Major—her joy was boundless.

Down the great staircase they went. Dawn was breaking—pink skies, clear light. And the faces of the party? Ah, how pale they looked! Especially the Major. Poor man—hours spent feeding Lady Clavering, flattering her, keeping her happy. His eyes were dark rings of bistre. His face was lined like a map of old wounds. Silver stubble glittered on his chin, like morning dew on a grave.

He stood there, silent, patient, exhausted—longing for bed, but staying because it was his duty. If martyrdom is admirable, then surely the gods smiled upon the Major’s endurance. Like the priests of Baal who cut themselves with knives, or the slaves of Mumbo Jumbo who tattooed themselves with hot skewers—he suffered for love, for duty, for friendship.

And when Lady Clavering insisted on leaving him at his door, he climbed into the back seat, polite to the last, resolute in his service.

As the carriage rolled away, Blanche looked out, wondering if she looked pale under her rose-colored hood. Arthur saw it clearly—she was yellow, drained. But Harry? For him, she was radiant. As bright as the sun rising over London.

Later, walking home, Arthur and Harry indulged in their usual habits: smoking cigars and mocking the nobility.

Arthur was sarcastic, dandyish, full of wit. He sneered at Lady John Turnbull’s French, Mrs. Slack Roper’s fake jewels, the old dandies, the young ones—everyone.

“You fire at everybody, Pen,” said Harry. “You’re awful.”

“Why don’t you take a shot at a brown wig?” Arthur asked. “You know whose I mean. It got into Lady Clavering’s carriage.”

“Under your uncle’s hat?” Harry asked, alarmed.

“My uncle is a martyr,” Arthur said. “He’s been working all night. He hates supper. He gets gout. He’s going home to pain and sleep. Shall I mock him? No. Not for Venice.”

“But why did he do it for your sake?” Harry asked.

“Boy,” Arthur said, striking a dramatic pose, “can you keep a secret? Swear you won’t tell?”

“What the deuce are you driving at?” Harry said, nervous.

Arthur ignored him. “Henry, friend of my youth—witness of my early follies—you may be dull at books, but you’re not without sense. You’ve courage, kindness—blush not, Henrico, you’ve plenty. Were I poor, I’d come to your purse. Were I sad, I’d pour my grief onto your shoulder—”

“Gammon, Pen,” Harry said. “Go on.”

“I would, Henrico, upon thy studs, and thy cambric worked by beauty! Know this: Arthur Pendennis, student-at-law, feels lonely. Care is furrowing his temples. Baldness is creeping in. I want money. I want to settle down. I’m thinking of marriage. A steady port and sherry character. Two maids, a man, a brougham, a house near the Parks—ha! What sayest thou?”

“But you haven’t got any money, Pen,” Harry said, still alarmed.

“Exactly,” Arthur said. “And that’s why I need help.”

The night at Gaunt House had left Major Pendennis utterly spent—so much so that the very thought of moving felt like a military campaign. He groaned his way to Buxton, where he sought solace in the mineral waters, as if hoping to soak away not just his fatigue but also the memory of his own existence. Parliament dissolved. Sir Francis Clavering and family vanished into the countryside, leaving behind only whispers of unfinished affairs. The town emptied out like a drained bathtub.

The season was over. Pen’s lawyer neighbours had taken off for circuit, their briefcases clutched like sacred relics. Fashionable folk had fled to the Continent or the Scottish moors—some for health, some for adventure, most just to escape the dullness of London. The Clubs were ghost towns; Pall Mall pavement was as lonely as a monk’s cell. Red jackets disappeared from Palace gates. St. James’s tradesmen were abroad, enjoying themselves—tailors with new mustaches sailing up the Rhine, bootmakers blushing at Ems or Baden, either meeting old customers or losing money at roulette tables. Clergymen preached to half-empty pews, where no notable sinner dared show his face. The band in Kensington Gardens had packed up its brass instruments, leaving only two or three ancient carriages crawling along the Serpentine. Clarence Bulbul, still chained to his Treasury clerk duties, rode through Rotten Row and declared it resembled the Arabian desert—himself a lone Bedouin wandering across the sands of ennui.

Warrington stuffed his carpetbag with Cavendish tobacco and headed for Norfolk, as he always did during the holidays. Pen remained behind, trapped by the demands of *The Pall Mall Gazette*, which he edited with the grace of a man who’d rather be elsewhere. Captain Shandon, the paper’s true owner, was currently recovering at Boulogne-sur-Mer, where he was said to be drinking water instead of brandy—and possibly regretting his life choices.

Now, despite Pen’s repeated claims that he was utterly jaded, bored beyond belief, and emotionally retired from all passion, the truth was that he was a remarkably healthy young man. He ate with gusto at least once a day, enjoyed company like a starving dog enjoys a bone, and could find joy in anything: a tavern parlor, a steamboat ride to Greenwich, a jaunt to Hampstead with Mr. Finucane, or a visit to Vauxhall Gardens, where he knew the great Simpson personally and once shook hands with the comic singer of the equestrian act.

He watched performances with a satirical eye, yet never without sympathy. He observed the revelers—the roistering youth, the delighted parents, the children clapping, the poor souls laughing too loudly, dancing till dawn, trying to forget their troubles with bread and cheap gin. He liked this—this human tapestry. He called it anthropology, his favorite pastime. Whether it was an old dowager flirting at a ball, a young beauty blushing, a guardsman flirting with a servant girl in the park, or little Tommy feeding ducks while his nurse looked on—Pen found charm in it all. And because his heart was clean, he could enjoy it deeply, secretly, even sadly—because, though surrounded by people, he often felt strangely alone.

“Look at me,” he once told Warrington, “I was in love so fiercely I think I burned out my flame. If I ever marry, it’ll be a marriage of reason—a sensible woman, well-bred, good-tempered, with a little money. Romance? That’s done. I’m old before my time—I’m proud of it.”

“Rubbish,” Warrington growled. “You claimed you were going bald last week, and bragged about it like it was a medal. Then you started using bear’s grease and now smell like a barber shop.”

“You’re Diogenes,” Pen replied, blushing slightly. “You want everyone to live in a tub like yourself. Violets smell better than stale tobacco, you grizzly cynic.”

But Pen, though pretending to care nothing for appearances, took great pains to look presentable. He wore tight boots and polished shoes like a man preparing for a royal audience—though he was merely a weary pilgrim of fashion.

It was on such a Friday evening in autumn, clear and crisp, that Pen, having just finished a brilliant leading article for the *Gazette* (one that would have made Captain Shandon proud—if he weren’t too busy drowning his sorrows in sea air), decided to cross the Thames and enjoy the fireworks at Vauxhall. He tucked his editor’s pass into his pocket—“Editor of *Pall Mall Gazette* and friend”—and paid for the bridge toll with a coin that might have been genuine.

The walk to the Gardens was lovely. Stars glittered above, watching the fireworks not yet launched. Before entering the enchanted realm of twenty thousand lamps, one passed through a dim, narrow passage guarded by wickets. On the walls were two illuminated holes where attendants collected money or admission orders.

Pen approached the second hole, where a gentleman and two ladies were already arguing.

The man, hat tilted at a rakish angle, wearing a shabby cloak with the flair of a dandy, was shouting.

“Bedad, sir, if ye doubt me honour, will ye obleege me by stipping out of that box, and——”

“Lor, Capting!” cried the elder lady.

“Don’t bother me,” snapped the man in the booth.

“And ask Mr. Hodgen himself, who’s in the gyardens, to let these leedies pass. Don’t be froightened, me dear madam, I’m not going to quarl with this gintleman, at anyreet before leedies. Will ye go, sir, and desoire Mr. Hodgen (whose orther I keem in with, and he’s me most intemate friend, and I know he’s goan to sing the ‘Body Snatcher’ here to-noight), with Captain Costigan’s compliments, to stip out and let in the leedies—for meself, sir, I’ve seen Vauxhall, and I scawrun any interfayrance on moi account: but for these leedies, one of them has never been there, and of should think ye’d harly take advantage of me misfartune in losing the ticket, to deproive her of her pleasure.”

“It ain’t no use, Captain. I can’t go about your business,” the check-taker said.

Captain Costigan swore a blue oath. The elder lady gasped: “Lor, ow provokin!”

The younger one looked up at him, said, “Never mind, Captain Costigan, I’m sure I don’t want to go at all. Come away, mamma.” But then, overwhelmed, she burst into tears.

“Me poor child!” the Captain cried. “Can ye see that, sir, and will ye not let this innocent creature in?”

“It ain’t my business,” the doorkeeper snapped.

Just then, Arthur Pendennis arrived, recognizing the Captain instantly.

“Don’t you know me, Captain? Pendennis!” he said, removing his hat and bowing.

“Me dear boy! Me dear friend!” Captain Costigan boomed, extending a hand. “A most unluckee conthratong! I had an order for two from Mr. Hodgen—he’s inside, singing the ‘Body Snatcher’ tonight—and I thought it admitted three. But I lost the ticket on the way. Now the leedies must go back—my daughter’s heart is broken!”

Arthur, ever generous, couldn’t refuse. He saw the girl’s tear-streaked face, her quivering lip, her quiet dignity beneath the grief. Even if it cost him his last shilling, he’d get her in.

“Is there any way I can help?” he asked kindly. “I have a ticket myself—it admits two. May I use it?”

The Prince of Fairoaks nearly offered to pay for the whole party. But instinct told him: don’t play the grand seigneur. So he handed his purse to Costigan, pulled out his ticket, and offered his arm to the elder lady—though “ladies” was stretching it. They wore bonnets, shawls, ribbons, and the younger one had a dainty foot peeking from under a modest grey dress. Still, Pen treated her with the same gallantry he’d show a queen.

“Fanny, take the gentleman’s arm,” the elder said. “Since you’re so kind—I’ve seen you come in at our gate, sir, and go to Captain Strong’s at No. 3.”

Fanny curtseyed and placed her small, shabby-gloved hand on Arthur’s arm. She wasn’t a child, nor quite a woman. Her tears had dried, her cheeks flushed, her eyes sparkled with gratitude.

Arthur gently placed his other hand over hers. “Fanny’s a pretty name,” he said. “And you know me?”

“We keep the lodge at Shepherd’s Inn, sir,” she said shyly. “I’ve never been to Vauxhall. Papa didn’t like me to go—and—and—O—O—law, how beautiful!”

She stepped back, dazzled by the sudden blaze of light—ten thousand lamps, more than any fairy tale could dream of. Pen smiled, touched by her wonder, and drew her closer.

“What would I not give for a little of this pleasure?” he mused aloud.

“Your purse, Pendennis, me dear boy,” Captain Costigan called from behind. “Will ye count it? It’s all roight—no—ye thrust in old Jack Costigan (he thrusts me, ye see, madam). Ye’ve been me preserver, Pen (I’ve known um since choildhood, Mrs. Bolton; he’s the proproietor of Fairoaks Castle, and many’s the cooper of clart I’ve dthrunk there with the first nobilitee of his neetive countee)—Mr. Pendennis, ye’ve been me preserver, and of thank ye; me daughtther will thank ye—Mr. Simpson, your humble servant, sir!”

If Pen was courteous, Captain Costigan was theatrical. He bowed to every singer, shouted “Bravo!” at the top of his lungs, and seemed to believe he was attending a coronation.

Meanwhile, Captain Costigan, noble descendant of Hibernian kings and chieftains, walked beside the ladies with dignified superiority—after all, they were merely housekeepers, not peers. But Pen had no such qualms. Miss Fanny Bolton didn’t make his bed or cook his meals. She was simply a charming girl, and he wouldn’t let go of her.

Fanny, meanwhile, glowed. She looked around at other couples, and thought: *My gentleman is handsomer than any of them.* There were rogues, clerks, dandies, and even Lord Colchicum, escorting Mademoiselle Caracoline, who spoke French with alarming force and leaned heavily on his lordship’s arm.

Colchicum was attending Mademoiselle Caracoline. Tom Tufthunt was attending Colchicum. When Don Juan climbs the wall, Leporello is never far behind.

When Pen and Fanny passed the Viscount’s party, Colchicum gave Arthur a dismissive glance, but Tufthunt waved cheerfully. “How are you, old boy?” he said, knowing full well he was being used as a glorified valet.

“That’s the rider from Astley’s,” Fanny whispered, pointing. “And who’s that old man?”

“That’s Lord Viscount Colchicum,” Pen said, protectively. He meant no harm. He just liked being protective.

Fanny was lovely—dark eyes, pearl-white teeth, lips redder than Madame Caracoline’s. Her laugh was pure, unlike the woman’s loud, vulgar cackle. Only recently had Fanny realized she was beautiful. A year ago, she was a clumsy, awkward girl, sneered at by her father and ignored at school. Now, blooming like a flower in a forgotten corner, she was radiant.

So they walked together through the gardens. Though London was empty, two million people still lingered—and among them, Henry Foker, Esq., pale, silent, hands in pockets, nodding ruefully at Pen.

Henry was trying to distract himself. But Blanche haunted him. He consulted fortune-tellers, who told him he loved a dark-haired woman who would make him happy—only to disappoint him when she wasn’t Blanche. At the concert, Mr. Momus sang his loudest songs, asked his silliest riddles—but no smile touched Foker’s lips. He didn’t hear a word.

Pen and Fanny stood nearby, listening.

“Why does that odd little man look so miserable?” Fanny asked.

“He’s crossed in love,” Pen said. “Isn’t that enough to ruin anyone?”

He looked down at her, noble and protective—like Egmont with Clara, Leicester with Amy.

“Crossed in love, is he?” Fanny sighed. “Poor gentleman.” She turned her eyes toward him with kindness—but Harry didn’t notice.

“How dy do, Mr. Pendennis!” a voice called.

It was Samuel Huxter, Clavering, in a white coat, red neckcloth, dingy shirt collar, glass buttons, trousers screaming “Look at me—I’m cheap!” He carried a cane and a pink-satin-clad lady on his arm.

“Pretty bobbish, thanky,” he said, wagging his head. “I say, Pendennis, you’ve been coming it strong since the row at Wapshot’s. Great author, hay? Saw your name in the *Morning Post*. Too much of a swell to come sup with an old friend? Charterhouse Lane tomorrow—some devilish good fellows from Bartholomew’s, stunning gin-punch. Here’s my card.”

He pulled it out with his teeth and handed it over.

“You’re exceedingly kind,” Pen said, taking it with a lofty bow. “But I have an engagement.”

Huxter shrugged. “Never mind, old fellow. If you’re passing, drop by. I’m mostly home Saturdays. Cheese cupboard’s open. Ta, ta.”

As he ran off toward the fireworks, Pen glanced at Fanny and smiled.

Then, hand in hand, they raced toward the pyrotechnics, Mrs. Bolton following, Captain Costigan lagging behind—too majestic to run. The fireworks exploded overhead, painting the sky in gold and crimson, while the city below slept, unaware of the tiny drama unfolding beneath the stars.

CHAPTER XLVIII. A Visit of Politeness (Revised for Humor)

Arthur Pendennis woke up one morning with the kind of vigor usually reserved for people who’ve just won the lottery, discovered a secret tunnel to Spain, or finally found their missing sock. No hostile messages from Mr. Huxter—no urgent financial demands, no existential dread about the state of his bank balance. Just peace. And a headache. But not the *kind* of headache that comes from overwork or moral decay. This was the sort you get after a night spent at Vauxhall, watching fireworks and thinking deep thoughts while sipping champagne like it’s your birthright.

He stirred in bed, stretched, and immediately began mentally reviewing the evening’s events—specifically, the charming little Fanny Bolton, whom he had met under circumstances so romantic they could have been lifted straight from a novel written by someone who’d never actually left London. “What a lovely creature,” he mused, blinking into the sunlight like a philosopher emerging from a cave. “So natural. So… unartificial. Unlike those ballroom butterflies who spend half their lives practicing how to trip over their own train while pretending not to care.”

He paused, then added, “Though I suppose Miss Blanche isn’t entirely without her charms either. But she does everything with such calculated grace—it’s like watching a clock tick backwards.”

Fanny, on the other hand, was pure spontaneity. She lived in a porter’s lodge, sang under the tutelage of an old man named Bows (who sounded suspiciously like a retired pirate), and had the kind of voice Arthur imagined angels might use when they’re trying to sound casual. “If her singing voice is as sweet as her speaking voice,” Arthur thought, “then I may be in trouble. Or at least in need of a new column for the *Pall Mall Gazette* titled ‘Why I Can’t Stop Thinking About a Girl Who Lives in a Doorway.’”

And so, inspired by both admiration and mild guilt (he didn’t know why he felt guilty—he hadn’t done anything wrong, except perhaps look too much like a prince when he wasn’t one), Arthur decided to pay a visit to Shepherd’s Inn. Not because he wanted to help Fanny learn to sing—though he did think that would be a noble pursuit—but because he wanted to study her, and also possibly write a book about her, which he’d title something dramatic like *The Rose That Grew in a Porter’s Lodge: A Tale of Love, Courage, and Minor Property Disputes*.

Also, he needed a break from genteel society. He’d grown tired of men who spoke only in monosyllables unless they were quoting Shakespeare, and women who giggled at nothing and yet expected you to believe they were witty. “I’m getting too old for this,” he muttered, pulling on his best white hat (which he wore even though it was Saturday, because style doesn’t take weekends off). “Too young for real men, too mature for party animals, and far too well-dressed for anyone who thinks a waistcoat should be worn as armor.”

Thus, armed with curiosity, a slightly-too-large umbrella, and a heart full of literary ambition, Arthur made his way to Shepherd’s Inn—where, if we’re honest, the odds of finding anything resembling high culture were about as good as finding a unicorn in a pub basement.

But lo! When he arrived at the wicket gate, there stood Fanny Bolton—blushing like a sunset, eyes wide as saucers, hands clasped together like she’d just seen God Himself descend from the clouds (and perhaps she had—because Arthur, in his white trousers and shiny boots, did look divine).

She called out, “Mamma! Mamma!” as if announcing the arrival of royalty—and honestly, what else could Arthur Pendennis be but a king of sorts? At least in the hearts of two women and four small children.

Mrs. Bolton, initially wary—“Who is this dandy? Is he here to collect rent? Or worse, ask about the bill-stamps?”—was quickly won over by Arthur’s charm, which was basically the equivalent of handing out free candy while wearing a top hat. He asked after her health, inquired about the undertaker business (which involved quite a few mutes, apparently), and expressed great interest in the Polwheedle and Tredyddlum railway shares—something that probably meant nothing to anyone but him.

He sat down on a child’s chair—because there were no proper chairs available, and because he liked to make a statement. The children laughed. Fanny blushed harder. One little girl, Betsy-Jane, said she wanted to go to the play too, which caused Fanny to snap, “Don’t bother!” and Mrs. Bolton to say, “Git-long, Betsy-Jane, do now,” sending the two youngest off to play near the statue of Shepherd the Great, who looked very stern and probably disapproved of all this nonsense.

Then came old Bows—dressed in clothes so ancient they seemed to have been woven by time itself. He appeared at the archway, saw Arthur sitting like a conquering hero atop a tiny chair, and froze.

“What! Mr. Bows?” Arthur boomed, as if greeting a long-lost uncle. “How d’you do, Bows? I was coming to see you!”

Bows blinked. “You were coming to see me?” he asked, sounding mildly alarmed.

“Yes,” Arthur replied, puffing out his chest. “I’ve decided to cultivate you. You’re a character. A genuine human being. I’ll put you in my next book.”

“Ah,” said Bows, nodding slowly. “That’s nice. I’ve always wanted to be in a book. Though I prefer the kind where you don’t have to wear a wig.”

They shook hands. Arthur offered tickets to the theatre. Mrs. Bolton nearly fainted with joy. Fanny clapped her hands. Even the cat, who had been sleeping on the roof, opened one eye and stared.

And thus ended the most important visit Arthur Pendennis had made all week—not because it changed history, or saved a soul, or even led to a romance—but because it reminded him that sometimes, the best stories aren’t written in ink, but in smiles, blushes, and the occasional awkward sit-down on a child’s chair.

And if that’s not comedy, then what is?

CHAPTER XLIX. In Shepherd’s Inn, our hero Pen—Arthur Pendennis, Esq.—arrived with the cheerful élan of a man who’d just survived a night of high drama and low finance, like some sort of literary James Bond with less gadgets and more debt. “How d’ye do, Mr. Bows?” he bellowed, as if announcing a royal arrival at a coronation. He saluted the elderly gentleman with the gusto of a man who’d just won a duel, though one could clearly see a faint blush creeping across Arthur’s cheeks—like a shy sunbeam trying to peek through storm clouds. This blush was mirrored instantly by Fanny Bolton, whose face lit up with such sudden color it looked as though someone had flicked her cheek with a rose petal dipped in hot sauce.

After the obligatory handshake—during which Bows, ever the dramatic ironist, pretended to accept Arthur’s claim that he was off to visit Captain Costigan’s chambers—the room fell into a silence so thick you could have cut it with a butter knife. Not just quiet, but *gloomy* quiet, like the kind you get when everyone knows they’re about to be caught sneaking cookies after dinner. Pen, ever the jester, tried to break the tension by rattling off an exaggerated reenactment of the previous night’s events: specifically, Captain Costigan’s failed attempt to charm his way out of a check-related disaster at Vauxhall Gardens.

It was not a good imitation. It was like watching a monkey try to play Beethoven on a piano made of cardboard. Nobody laughed. Mrs. Bolton, bless her cotton-wool heart, didn’t even know what part Pen was playing—was he the check-taker? The captain? A confused pigeon? Fanny tried a tiny, nervous giggle, like a mouse attempting to whistle. Old Mr. Bows, meanwhile, looked as glum as a funeral dirge played on a fiddle tuned by a depressed badger. Pen’s voice faltered mid-sentence, then died completely—like a candle snuffed by a sad ghost. Darkness returned, thicker than before.

Then came the sound of a ticket-porter’s boots clinking on the cobbles outside. Everyone heard it. It was the only thing that broke the silence—like a clock ticking in a tomb.

“You were coming to see me, sir,” said Mr. Bows, suddenly regaining his theatrical bearing. “Won’t you walk up to my chambers? They are rather high up—but—”
“Oh! I live in a garret myself,” Pen interrupted, puffing up like a peacock. “Shepherd’s Inn is twice as cheerful as Lamb Court.”
“I knew you had third-floor apartments,” Bows replied, “and was going to say—please don’t take this as discourteous—that the air up three pair of stairs is wholesomer for gentlemen than the air of a porter’s lodge.”

“Sir!” Pen snapped, his inner flame reigniting. “Will you permit me to choose my society without—?”
“You were so polite as to say you were honouring my humble domicile,” Bows said, voice dropping to a mournful baritone, “shall I show you the way? Mr. Pendennis and I are old friends, Mrs. Bolton—very old acquaintances; we crossed paths at the dawn of his life.”

He gestured dramatically toward the door, finger trembling like a leaf in a gale, hat held aloft like a flag of surrender. His words were stiff and formal, borrowed from a lifetime of listening to actors on stage. But he wasn’t acting. Not really. Pen knew that. He just didn’t care. “Come along, sir,” he said, “as you are so very pressing.” And with a final farewell to Fanny—pressing her hand like a man who might never see her again—he vanished into the stairwell.

Mrs. Bolton immediately declared, “What a nice young man, to be sure!”
“D’you think so, ma?” Fanny asked, eyes wide.
“I was a-thinkin’ who he was like,” Mrs. Bolton mused, peering through the curtain. “When I was at the Wells with Mrs. Serle, there was a young gentleman from the city, used to come in a tilbry, white hat, the very image of him—only his whiskers was black, and Mr. P.’s is red.”
“Law, ma!” Fanny gasped. “They are a most beautiful hawburn!”

And thus began a lengthy monologue about a sugar-baker from Omerton who once courted a dancer named Emly Budd (who danced Columbine in *Arleykin Ornpipe, or the Battle of Navarino*), got married at St. Bartholomew’s Church in a private ceremony, and now patronized the Manshing-House Ball for Washywomen’s Asylum. Also mentioned: Lady Mirabel, Captain Costigan’s daughter, who was “profeshnl,” according to Mrs. Bolton, “as all very well know.”

Meanwhile, Fanny, still blushing from the earlier kiss, sat by the window like a statue waiting for a miracle. Her mother left for market, leaving her alone. Fanny watched the door like a hawk. Then—movement! She perked up. But no. It was Pen, followed by Bows. They passed under the archway. Bows bowed. Didn’t stop. Didn’t speak. Just… went.

Minutes later, Bows returned alone.
“Where’s your Ma, dear?” he asked.
“I don’t know,” Fanny snapped. “I don’t follow Ma’s steps wherever she goes, I suppose, Mr. Bows.”
“Am I my mother’s keeper?” Bows sighed, voice cracking with melancholy.

Then, like a man offering candy to a classroom full of children, he produced gingerbread cakes for the two youngest Bolton girls—Betsy-Jane and Ameliar-Ann—who were pretending to read a picture-book upside-down. Their reward? Gingerbread medals. They scampered off to discuss their spoils in the courtyard.

Fanny, meanwhile, picked up her sewing, though her mind was elsewhere. She was angry. Excited. Confused. She stitched like a woman possessed, her needle flying faster than her thoughts.

Bows sat facing the entrance, as if expecting someone—or something—to appear. But nobody did. Only Mrs. Bolton returned from the market, and found Bows where she expected to find Pen.

The reader may now guess who the guest was. (Spoiler: It was Pen.)

Upstairs, in Bows’s neat little room—neat, that is, compared to Costigan’s chaotic war zone—Pen and Bows met again. Costigan, still in his bedclothes (or perhaps just *pretending* to be dressed), had already emerged from his chamber and remained throughout the visit. He smelled, incidentally, like a bottle of whisky that had been opened and then forgotten in a drawer. A rich, heady perfume of otto-of-whisky—whatever that is—clung to him like a second skin. His hand shook so violently it was a wonder he hadn’t accidentally shaved off half his chin.

Bows’s room, in contrast, was tidy as a schoolmaster’s desk. Books. Manuscript music. A lithograph of Miss Fotheringay as Mrs. Haller, signed in messy script. Lady Mirabel’s handwriting, by contrast, was elegant—she’d worked hard at it since marriage. But Bows preferred the old style. He kept a single note from her, written in reply to a song he’d composed and dedicated to her. It was tucked away like a sacred relic.

Now he was teaching Fanny to sing and write—just as he once taught Emily. There was something deeply human about Bows: he attached himself to things. When Emily left, he found a substitute—like a man who loses a leg and learns to walk with a crutch. Or a sailor who, shipwrecked, latches onto a raft. Latude, imprisoned in the Bastille, grew fond of a mouse. Some men fall in love with poodles. Bows had fallen in love with Fanny—and now here was Pen, back in town, chasing her like a man who’d just discovered romance was still legal.

Costigan, oblivious, thought he was the reason for Pen’s visit. He was delighted. He promised himself he would return the favor—though he already owed more debts than a pawnbroker’s ledger. He chatted amiably about Sir Charles and Lady Mirabel’s trip to Baden-Baden, the Grand Duke’s hospitality, and the glorious festivities at Dublin Castle, where the Earl of Portansherry held a Viceroygal Coort (a term so grand it sounded like a sandwich).

As Costigan droned on, Pen remembered the past—the first love, the bridge talk, the kindness of Bows. He felt warm toward both men. He shook hands cordially, even warmly, as he prepared to leave.

But then—Bows limped down the stairs, determined to follow him.
Pen’s patience snapped. *Confound him!* What does he mean, dogging me? He burst out laughing—loud, bitter, self-satisfied—as he walked into the Strand. But the laugh didn’t feel honest. It felt like a cover-up. And somewhere deep inside, Arthur Pendennis blushed—not at the joke, but at himself.

He tried to escape his thoughts. He climbed the Panorama stairs, panting like a dog in a heatwave. But Care was already waiting at the top. He went to the Club, wrote a long, witty letter home—full of sarcasm and cleverness—never mentioning Vauxhall or Fanny. He couldn’t focus on books. Jawkins, the only man in London who seemed to enjoy conversation, couldn’t hold his attention. Nothing worked.

On his way home, he passed a comic theatre. The sign screamed: *Stunning Farce!* *Roars of Laughter!* *Good Old English Fun and Frolic!* In vermilion letters, like a cry from the heavens.

He went in. Saw Mrs. Leary in male drag. Tom Horseman as a woman. Horseman’s performance was hideous. Mrs. Leary’s ankles? Meaningless. But Pen laughed—bitterly—thinking of how much he’d once swooned over her. That night in London… how long ago it seemed. Not just years. Centuries.

And yet, as he walked home, the laughter faded. The world felt heavier. Because love, it seemed, was not just pleasure. It was also pain. And he had just begun to understand it.

CHAPTER L. The Temple Gardens, once a haunt of roses and romantic quarrels—those innocent white and red blooms that sparked civil wars in Shakespeare’s day—have long since been abandoned by fashion. According to the learned author of *The Handbook of London*, even the hardiest rose has given up on this smog-choked air. Few who live in the surrounding buildings care whether any flowers bloom there anymore; most pass through only to reach their chambers, their briefcase clutched tighter than a lover’s hand, their thoughts as dry as legal briefs.

The clerks don’t carry bouquets, nor do they pause for poetry. Only the occasional lawyer with a constitutional walk might glance at the garden—but not out of reverence for York or Lancaster, nor for the ghostly echoes of Sir Roger de Coverley or Mr. Spectator strolling along the path. No, these days, it's mostly antiquarians and literary dreamers who imagine Goldsmith musing under the summer-house roof, perhaps pondering his next *Citizen of the World* or the tailor’s latest ill-fitting suit. Or maybe he’s just trying to ignore the dunning letter from Mr. Newbery.

And then, like a sudden burst of inspiration—or perhaps just a man trying to avoid thinking about money—comes Dr. Johnson, rolling forward in a snuff-coloured suit and a wig that needs both powder and a good scolding from a barber. His Scotch lackey trails behind, slightly unsteady from port wine consumed at the Mitre. “Come home,” says the Great Doctor, “and take tea with Miss Williams.” Ah, kind fancy! Sir Roger and Mr. Spectator may be fictional, but they’re no less real to us than the actual men who once walked here. Poets live longer than politicians, especially when they're remembered fondly.

Arthur Pendennis, being of a poetic and literary turn (and decidedly not interested in law), was likely lost in such musings one evening after the events of the previous chapter. He found himself wandering into the Temple Gardens, not because he’d ever planned to, but because the quiet Sunday night seemed to whisper: *Walk. Think. Escape.*

On Sundays, the Temple is serene. Chambers are empty. The bigwigs are off hosting dinner parties in Belgravia or Tyburnia, sipping claret and flirting with daughters named Ermine. Young barristers attend these feasts, while the uninvited settle for joint and half-pint at the Club, trading Circuit jokes like seasoned veterans. Only poor Mr. Cockle remains, sick in bed, fed gruel by his laundress. Mr. Toodle plays flute solos from the second floor. And young Tiger, student extraordinaire, emits cigar smoke like a volcano and leaves dishes piled outside his door—clearly a man of refined taste.

But wait—vacation time! Everyone’s gone. Except Arthur Pendennis.

Perhaps solitude drew him in. Or perhaps it was just that he’d had a rather lonely dinner at a tavern nearby and fancied some fresh air. So, off he went, walking slowly down the gravel paths, past groups of contented citizens enjoying the calm, the river, the sunset. After a few minutes, he grew tired and ducked into one of the summer-houses at the end of the main walk.

What did he think about?

The sky was clear. The Thames sparkled. The chimneys across the way were silent—no smoke, just peace. Warehouses glowed rosy in the light, as if they’d taken a bath for the Sabbath. Steamers rushed up and down, full of holiday folk. Church bells rang out, summoning prayers. It was the kind of evening Arthur might have known as a child—walking with his mother on the terrace at home, the sun setting behind the Clavering elms and the village church.

Was it memory? Or just the blush of twilight? He tapped his foot to the bells, dusted his boots with his handkerchief, stood up, stamped his foot, and declared: “No, by Jove, I’ll go home.”

That decision suggested an internal battle—whether to stay, or flee. A temptation, perhaps.

He stepped out of the summer-house—and nearly tripped over two tiny children, barely knee-high, trotting along with long blue shadows stretching eastward.

“Oh!” said one.

“Missa Pendennis!” chirped the other, with a mischievous chuckle.

Arthur looked down—and saw them: Mesdemoiselles Ameliar-Ann and Betsy-Jane, his little friends from the day before.

He blushed. Then, impulsively, he picked up Ameliar-Ann, tossed her gently into the air, and kissed her.

She screamed.

Which brought forth two ladies—Mrs. Bolton in a scarlet Caledonian Cashmere and black silk, and Miss Fanny Bolton in yellow scarf, sprigged muslin, and parasol—looking every inch the genteel matron and lady.

Fanny didn’t speak. But her eyes lit up—brighter than any window in Paper Buildings.

Mrs. Bolton, after scolding Betsy-Jane, said: “Lor sir—how very odd we should meet you here! I hope you’ve got your health well, sir. Ain’t it odd, Fanny, that we should meet Mr. Pendennis?”

Ah, coincidence. That’s all it was.

Earlier, Arthur had casually mentioned how pleasant the gardens were on Sundays. And now, lo and behold, fate had arranged a reunion—just like those polite encounters in novels where lovers meet in shrubberies or at seaside piers. Wasn’t it funny? Hadn’t you seen it happen before? At the Hall? At the Lodge?

Arthur looked dashing—white trousers, white hat, multicolored neckcloth, gold chains, shirt-studs gleaming. He was practically a prince of the blood. How he carried himself! How he blushed! Some unseen observer might have whispered: *Has anyone ever looked so fine?*

And indeed, the children had been playing at being him. Ameliar-Ann, sticking her fingers into her pinafore like Pen did with his waistcoat, had announced: “Now, Bessy-Jane, I’ll be Missa Pendennis.” Fanny laughed until she cried, kissing her sister for the performance.

Arthur was touched—especially when he saw how happy Fanny was to see him embrace the child.

But Fanny looked pale. Worn. Arthur asked why.

“I was awake all night,” she said, blushing.

“Put out her candle,” Mrs. Bolton interjected. “Hordered her to sleep and leave off readin’.”

“Reading?” Arthur asked, amused. “What was so interesting?”

“Oh, it’s so beautiful!” Fanny sighed. “‘Walter Lorraine.’ I hate Neaera—Neaera—I don’t know how to say it. But I love Leonora, and Walter… oh, how dear he is!”

How had Fanny discovered the novel—and that Arthur wrote it?

She remembered everything he’d said the night before: that he wrote books and articles. What books? She became determined to find out.

First, she charmed Captain Costigan—smiling, helping with dinner, offering to mend his shirts (which had holes so large she could stick her face through one). She danced around his room, mixed him a tumbler of whisky (and tasted a drop), made him sing an Irish song in his rich, quavering voice—fancying herself the little siren.

Then came her question: “Do you know Arthur Pendennis? Does he write a book called ‘Walter Lorraine’?”

Costigan, though not a literary type, knew the name. “Oh yes,” he said. “They call him ‘Walter Lorraine’ at the Back Kitchen. Bludyer calls him a confounded coxcomb. Hoolan wonders why Doolan doesn’t kick him.”

So Fanny went to the library—several libraries, actually. Some had the book. One wouldn’t let her borrow it without a sovereign. She didn’t have one. She returned crying.

“I was so afraid someone would take it while I was away,” she said, cheeks glowing.

Arthur was flattered—deeply moved by her innocence, her passion.

“Do you like it?” he asked. “If you come to my chambers—no, I’ll bring you one. No, I’ll send you one. Good night. Thank you, Fanny. God bless you. I mustn’t stay. Good-bye.”

He pressed her hand, nodded to her mother, and strode out—quickening his pace, talking to himself.

“Dear, dear little thing,” he murmured. “Darling little Fanny! You’re worth them all. I wish Shandon were back. I’d go home to my mother. I mustn’t see her. I won’t. I won’t, so help me—”

As he ran, people turned to watch. He collided with a small old man—Mr. Bows.

“Your very umble servant, sir,” Bows said, bowing sarcastically.

“I wish you a good day,” Arthur replied, sulkily. “Don’t detain me. I’m in a hurry. Good evening.”

Bows thought something was wrong. “Where are your rooms?” he demanded. “They told Bolton they were going to church. They weren:’t there. They’re in your chambers. They mustn’t stay there, Mr. Pendennis.”

“Damn it, sir!” Arthur snapped. “Come and see if they are. Here’s the court. Come in.”

Bows bowed and followed.

They weren’t there.

Later, as the gardens closed, the women walked away sadly, the children dragging behind. They passed under the lamp-post in Lamb Court, looked up at Pendennis’s chamber window—where a light soon appeared—and then left.

Bows inspected the room—bare, littered, unchanged since last seen. “Shall I look in the bedrooms?” Arthur asked bitterly. “See if I’ve hidden the girls in the coal-hole?”

“Your word is sufficient,” Bows said solemnly. “You say they’re not here. I believe you. And I hope they never were.”

Arthur bristled. “You’re choosing my acquaintances for me, sir. You suppose I’d ruin someone’s reputation. I remember you, Mr. Bows. I owe you kindness. But this persecution—this watching me—”

He paused, realizing he’d slipped.

“You came to steal something,” Bows said quietly.

“To visit poor old Bows? Or Mrs. Bolton?” Arthur scoffed. “O fie! Such a fine gentleman as Arthur Pendennis doesn’t condescend to sit in a laundress’s kitchen unless he has reasons of his own. My belief is—you came to steal a girl’s heart, ruin it, and cast it aside.”

“It’s sport to you,” Bows continued, “but what is it to the poor? Toys you play with, then throw away. I know your kind. Selfish. Arrogant. Proud. What does it matter to your lordship if a poor man’s daughter is broken? You must have your pleasures. We must pay for them. What are we made for, but that?”

Arthur laughed—a little too sharply. “Yes, I’m an aristocrat. In a palace three flights up. With a carpet nearly as handsome as yours, Mr. Bows. My life is grinding the people? Ruining virgins? This is comedy. Job Thornberry slapping his chest, asking my Lord how dare he trample on an honest man. But in real life, Mr. Bows—when I work as hard as you do—how can you talk about tyranny?”

“You were different once,” Bows said. “Honest. Impetuous. You liked me because I was ready to ruin myself for a woman.”

“And now?” Arthur asked.

“And now,” Bows replied, “you want a woman to ruin herself for you.”

He spoke of Fanny—how she’d become obsessed with novels, how her mind was filled with romance, how she barely noticed she lived in a kitchen. “I’ve taught her,” Bows said. “She’s talented. Winning. I’m fond of her. I’m a lonely old man. I have only her. Don’t take her from me, Mr. Pendennis. Please.”

His voice cracked.

Arthur felt it—more than sarcasm, more than threat.

“Indeed,” he said kindly, “you do me wrong. I never meant harm to Fanny. I met her by chance. I have no intentions—none.”

“None?” Bows said. “You know she’s foolish. Her mother is foolish. You met her in the gardens. She scorned me yesterday when she read your book. What am I good for but to be laughed at? A deformed old fiddler, wearing threadbare clothes, earning bread at an alehouse? You wear scent. You wear rings. You dine with great people. Who gives a crust to old Bows? I might have been great. I wrote plays, poems, music. Nobody listened. I loved a woman—she laughed at me. Now I’m alone. Don’t take this girl from me.”

Arthur sighed. “I swear, I never meant to affect her. I didn’t realize I had—until tonight. And then, I was running from temptation. When I heard the bells, I thought of home—of my mother, of pure women there. I was fleeing danger, seeking strength from God.”

Silence.

Then Bows softened. They shook hands. He apologized for misunderstanding. Arthur squeezed his hand warmly.

As they parted, Arthur said: “I’ve given a promise. I hope you trust it.”

“Amen to that prayer,” Bows said, and walked slowly down the stairs.

And so the evening ended—not with scandal, but with sorrow, honesty, and a quiet vow.

CHAPTER LI. The Happy Village Again—Or, How a Little Gossip Ruined a Gentleman

Early in this history, we’ve mentioned the little town of Clavering—nestled near Fairoaks like a well-behaved sheepdog beside a noble hound—and its inhabitants, whose lives were so uneventful that even their misfortunes seemed to happen in slow motion. We didn’t linger long over them before, because what’s there to say about people who live by the rhythms of the harvest and the church bell? But now, dear reader, we must return—because sometimes the most harmless villages become the stage for tragedies more absurd than any play at Drury Lane.

Take, for instance, Mr. Samuel Huxter—the same gentleman we met at Vauxhall, where he was trying to impress a lady with his knowledge of opera and his ability to name every wine on the menu. When not in London, Sam was a man of taste and wit in Clavering, where he’d descend upon friends with the air of a visiting philosopher, regaling them with stories from Bartholomew’s and the latest scandals from Mayfair. He’d been known to quote Byron while sipping tea and then immediately ask if anyone had seen the new issue of *The Times*.

Then there was Mr. Hobnell—the young man whom Arthur Pendennis once thrashed after a quarrel involving a certain Fotheringay affair (a story best left buried, like bad cheese). At the time, Hobnell was a grammar school boy, but already showing signs of becoming a gentleman: he knew where the tamarind pots were kept in Mrs. Huxter’s surgery, could scent his handkerchief with rosewater without spilling it on the carpet, and once saved a cat from a tree using only a walking stick and a dramatic flourish.

He also fell in love with Miss Sophy Huxter—Sam’s sister—and married her after his father passed away. That father had been a man of ambition: he tore down the old farmhouse, built a flashy whitewashed mansion with a piano in the drawing room (a scandal!), kept a pack of harriers, and insisted on being called “Squire” as though he’d inherited the title from King George himself. When the son took over, the family officially became “county gentry”—which meant they now owned a dog named “Buck,” wore waistcoats with embroidery, and believed they were above the local butcher.

Sam Huxter, naturally, liked to brag about his brother-in-law’s grandeur. “Hobnell’s got horses!” he’d announce at dinner parties. “And hounds! And a house with a veranda! He’s practically a duke.” His comrades would nod sagely, pretending to care, while secretly wondering if the piano really worked.

Every year, when Mrs. Hobnell was busy feeding babies or changing diapers (which she did with the grace of a queen), Squire Hobnell would come up to London for a week-long spree. He’d stay at the Tavistock Hotel, wear a cravat with a knot so tight it looked like it might strangle him, and spend evenings at Ascot, the theatres, and Vauxhall, always accompanied by Sam, who would narrate the events like a Greek historian covering a battle.

“Did you see the way he danced?” Sam would whisper. “It was like a bear trying to waltz—graceful, yet terrifying.”

When Hobnell returned to Clavering, he’d bring back a new bonnet for his wife and a shawl made of something suspiciously like goat hair. Then he’d vanish into the countryside, ready to hunt, drink brandy, and forget the city—except for the occasional letter to Sam, which usually began: “Dear Sam, I have just shot three pheasants and one rabbit. Also, the weather is frightfully cold. What news from London?”

Sam, meanwhile, sent baskets of game, cream, and gossip—especially gossip about Pen.

Ah, yes—Arthur Pendennis.

After their meeting at Vauxhall, Pen had vowed never to see little Fanny again. Not out of cruelty, mind you, but because he felt guilty. So he threw himself into work—writing scathing reviews for the *Pall Mall Gazette*, tearing apart poems and novels with such ferocity that one author wept into his soup. He dined alone at the Polyanthus Club, where the silence was so thick it could be cut with a knife. He went to the theatre, but found the audience laughing at jokes that seemed ridiculous to him. One scene, in particular—a man falling into a tub of water—made him think: “Why do they find this funny? It’s tragic. It’s pathetic. It’s… damp.”

Still, he needed distraction. So he headed to the Back Kitchen, his old haunt, where Bows played the piano and the air smelled of stale beer and regret. He sat down, ordered a whisky-and-water, and began to reflect on life, love, and how badly he’d handled things.

And then—of course—Mr. Huxter walked in.

Huxter, fresh from the theatre, greeted everyone with a wink and a grin. He saw Pen, nodded familiarly, and extended a hand covered in crumbs from a previous meal. Pen shook it, feeling awkward. He’d been harsh at Vauxhall, and now he regretted it. But Huxter, blissfully unaware of anything beyond his own charm, began chatting cheerfully.

“Jolly night at Vauxhall—wasn’t it?” he said, winking again. “I’m glad you enjoyed it. You were dancing with a lovely girl—what was her name? She had such nice muslin!”

Pen froze.

“That was *not* my fault,” he muttered.

“You’re quite the ladies’ man,” Huxter continued, oblivious. “I heard you were seen at a party with some young thing—must be hard to keep track of all of them.”

Pen stood up.

“Have I asked you any questions about yourself, Mr. Huxter?” he said coldly.

“No, no—I didn’t mean offence,” Huxter stammered. “Hang it, you’re acting like a grumpy old owl!”

“I beg your pardon,” Pen said, voice rising. “But I asked you not to speak to me. I asked you not to recognize me. And I’ll thank you to remember that.”

He turned and left, leaving behind a room full of laughter—because Huxter’s friends thought the whole thing was hilarious. The president of the club had to shout: “Silence, gentlemen! Silence for *The Body Snatcher!*”

Pen walked home, heart pounding. He wanted to fight someone. Or scream. Or throw something. But instead, he went to bed—and slept worse than ever.

A few days later, Sam Huxter wrote a letter to Hobnell in the country. It was full of outrage.

“Arthur Pendennis is a disgrace!” he wrote. “A Don Juan! A rogue! I saw him at Vauxhall, dancing with a poor innocent girl—probably stealing her virtue! I’ve learned from an Irish fellow at my club that she’s from a good family. I think I should warn her father. This is terrible. Terrible.”

He signed off with thanks for the rabbits and a request for more clouted cream.

Now, once a year, there was a christening at the Warren. On this occasion, it was a little girl named Myra Lucretia—named after two godmothers, Miss Portman and Mrs. Pybus. After reading Sam’s letter, Hobnell told his wife, who told her friends, and soon the whole village was buzzing.

Mrs. Pybus, ever the moral compass, declared that men were born liars and quoted poetry about “deceivers.” Mrs. Portman blamed Pen’s upbringing: “His mother spoiled him! He read too many novels! He forgot Doctor Portman’s teachings!”

Captain Glanders, the local dragoon, whistled during dinner. “Ah, young lovers,” he said. “Always getting into trouble.” Mrs. Glanders called him a brute and sent the girls out of the room.

Mr. Simcoe remained calm—but only because he’d already decided Pen was a villain. He hadn’t read any of Pen’s books—he wouldn’t dream of it—but he knew that young men who wrote novels were doomed.

Even the chapel-of-ease held a special sermon on the dangers of London and the sin of reading fiction. The congregation didn’t wait to hear the truth—they assumed Pen was guilty. Who would fling the first stone? Everyone.

Meanwhile, Mrs. Pendennis received an anonymous letter—written in scriptural tones, accusing her son of great wickedness. She fainted. Her daughter Laura read it and blushed—not with shame, but rage.

“It isn’t true,” she said.

“It *is* true,” cried Helen, furious. “You refused him! You broke my heart! You led him into sin! Now he’s with that woman—don’t speak to me!”

She ran to Dr. Portman’s house, half-crazed.

Doctor Portman, seeing the letter, recognized the handwriting—though he pretended otherwise. He tried to comfort her: “It’s anonymous. It could be false. We must hear Arthur’s side.”

But Helen saw through him.

“You think he’s guilty,” she said. “You know he is. Oh, why did I leave him? Why did I let him go?”

She collapsed.

Dr. Portman sent a letter to Pen: “Dear Arthur, I fear rumours are spreading about you. Please repent. Break off this fatal connection. Come home. Your mother is broken-hearted.”

And what of Laura?

Her heart was torn. She loved Arthur. She knew he wasn’t a monster. But she also knew that if the rumors were true—if he had truly done such a thing—then she would lose both him and her mother forever.

So she sat in silence, thinking: *What if it’s true? What if I am the reason for all this pain?*

And thus, the happy village of Clavering—where nothing ever happened—became the epicenter of a drama so absurd, so tragic, so perfectly English, that even the cows paused to listen.

CHAPTER LII. Which Had Very Nearly Been the Last of the Story—But Wasn’t, Thanks to a Few Cigars and a Good Irishman

Doctor Portman’s letter—full of moral thunder, pastoral concern, and enough religious advice to fill a small cathedral—was duly posted off to London, like a solemn missive from the heavens. The good doctor then set about calming Mrs. Pendennis, who was currently in a state of agitation that could only be described as “a woman who has just discovered her son might not be entirely beyond reproach.” He assured her (though he didn’t quite believe it himself) that the reply would be satisfactory—morally speaking, at least. And while Helen Pendennis longed to march into town and personally warn her son of his sins, the apothecary forbade it—on grounds of health, not virtue. So she remained confined to her sofa, with Laura by her side, silent as a saint, patient as a stone, and twice as noble.

Unfortunately for everyone involved, Arthur Pendennis never read this sermon of conscience until weeks later—by which time the damage had been done, the fever had begun, and the world had already decided that Pen was either a man of principle or a complete fool, depending on whether you’d seen him last night at Vauxhall or this morning in bed with a temperature.

Each day passed without word from Arthur, and each day made Helen sicker. Laura, meanwhile, bore the weight of it all with the quiet strength of someone who knows that duty is the only thing worth keeping when love turns cold. She prayed—not loudly, not dramatically—but in the stillness of her room, where God, perhaps, heard better than any human ear ever could. And though we can’t describe her virtues in detail—because such things are too sacred for public display, like a rose blooming in silence—we will say this: a good woman is the most beautiful flower in the garden of life. Let us admire her silently, as one admires moonlight on water. And let us mourn when she fades—not because she deserved it, but because the world is crueler than it should be.

Meanwhile, back in Lamb Court, Arthur Pendennis was doing his best to forget Fanny Bolton—though forgetting her was like trying to stop a river by holding your breath. He resolved not to see her again. No more Vauxhall. No more lingering glances. No more dreams of a girl who lived in a porter’s lodge and thought he was a prince. He tried distraction: work, walking, riding, smoking cigars like they were oxygen. But nothing worked. The image of Fanny—her eyes, her voice, her ridiculous little laugh—kept returning, like a ghost haunting a poorly ventilated room.

So he overdid it. He worked too hard. He walked too far. He ate too much, drank too much, smoked too much, and finally collapsed—literally—into a feverish heap on his own bed. And thus began the tragic comedy of bachelorhood in chambers.

Ah, the bachelor’s life! A condition so dire, so lonely, so utterly absurd, that it deserves its own series of satirical illustrations by Cruikshank or Leech—preferably with captions like *“The Bachelor’s Breakfast: A Study in Despair”* or *“The Shirt That Refused to Button: A Tragedy in One Act.”*

Imagine: the black kettle hissing in midsummer, the fire gone out at Christmas, the laundress long gone, leaving behind only the ghost of her presence and a pile of unpaid bills on the table. You enter, shivering, like a condemned man arriving at his execution, and must first light the fire, find the coal, clean the hearth, and then—only then—attempt to eat breakfast. All before you’ve even opened your newspaper.

Then there’s dinner. The shirt, alas, has no buttons. You try to wear it anyway, looking like a man who’s been attacked by a tailor and left for dead. Or worse: you return from a merry holiday at a country house full of pretty faces and warm embraces, only to find your portmanteau at the barber’s, your candle flickering like a dying ember, and your room filled with Christmas bills—each one a personal insult from the universe.

And if you’re ill? Oh, then it’s truly tragic. To lie in chambers, fevered and alone, with no one to bring you water, no hand to smooth your pillow, no kind voice to say, “You’ll be alright.” Instead, you serve yourself medicine by the glow of your own watch, and spend the night listening to your own thoughts—some of which may be about Fanny Bolton, others about how badly you need a servant.

This was Arthur’s fate. After a week of self-denial and excess, he fell ill. One night, he went to bed feeling unwell. The next morning, he woke up worse. His only visitors were the laundress and the Printer’s Devil from the *Pall Mall Gazette*, whom he managed to satisfy with half a column of copy—though not nearly enough to save the paper from looking like it had been written by a drunken monkey.

Mr. Finucane, bless him, came rushing up to check on the invalid. Seeing how bad Arthur looked, he took matters into his own hands. He wrote the entire political section of the Gazette—complete with passionate odes to Ireland, glowing tributes to Rafferty the painter (who, according to Finucane, was clearly being wronged by the Academy), and news that Mr. Rooney, M.P., was almost certainly going to be appointed Governor of Barataria. Shandon, the editor, reading the paper from Boulogne-sur-Mer, laughed and said to his wife, “Look here, Mary, Jack’s at it again.” And indeed, Jack was. He was also, for a few days, Arthur’s nurse.

Finucane offered to skip his weekend to stay with Arthur, but Arthur refused. “I’m fine,” he insisted. “I can manage myself.” Which was true—up to a point. He could manage himself, but only until he couldn’t.

On Friday night, after finishing the paper, Finucane told Captain Costigan about Arthur’s illness. Two days later, the Captain visited Lamb Court.

He found Mrs. Flanagan, the laundress, weeping in the sitting-room. Arthur was in a terrible state. She had taken brandy to calm herself—three times, possibly four—and was now trembling, red-eyed, and deeply ashamed. When she tried to care for Arthur, he snapped at her. “Don’t come near me!” he growled. This broke her heart. She wept harder. She reached for the bottle again. The Captain, seeing this, scolded her like a father scolding a child who’s been caught stealing cake. “You’re killing yourself!” he said. “And what good does it do anyone?”

Arthur, meanwhile, was in a fevered state, but delighted to see the Captain. He called out eagerly, “Captain! Come in! Thank you for coming!” He made the Captain sit down, though he himself was tossing about in bed like a fish on a hook. They talked about the Back Kitchen, about Vauxhall, about when they’d go again—then Arthur asked, “How is little Fanny?”

That question, simple as it was, sent a tremor through the whole story.

Fanny, poor dear, had returned home on Sunday evening, her heart broken. She had seen Arthur light his lamp—just as Bows had watched. Then Bows came back to his room, face as long as a fiddlestick. He didn’t speak. He just stared into space. That night, Fanny couldn’t sleep. She kept the book under her pillow—*Walter Lorraine*—and felt for it in the dark. Her children stirred early. She was furious with Bows. Yet, despite everything, she went to his room the next day.

“I saw Mr. Pendennis last night,” Bows said.

“Did you?” Fanny replied, glaring. “I thought you did.”

“I’ve liked you ever since we came here,” Bows continued. “You were a child when I arrived. You used to like me—until three or four days ago. Until you met this gentleman.”

“And now,” Fanny said, “you’re going to say bad things about him, aren’t you? Go ahead. It’ll make me like you better.”

“No,” Bows said. “I think he’s a good young man. Honest.”

“Honest?” Fanny shouted. “Then why won’t he come see me? Why won’t he talk to me? You know he loves me!”

“He does,” Bows said gently. “But he can’t marry you.”

Fanny’s defiance turned to despair. “Why not?” she whispered. “What have I done?”

Bows explained: Arthur was a gentleman. He had a future. He couldn’t marry a girl from a porter’s lodge. It wouldn’t be right. It wouldn’t be fair. It would ruin his prospects. And a decent girl wouldn’t want to ruin a man’s life for love.

Fanny wept. “I don’t care,” she sobbed. “I’d give up everything. I’d work for him. I’d learn. I’d become worthy. I’d die for him.”

She told her mother everything. She spoke of Pauline in *The Lady of Lyons*, of how she stayed faithful to Claude. “If everyone admires Pauline,” she said, “why can’t a gentleman love a poor girl? Not that Arthur loves me—I’m not worthy of him. Only a princess is worthy of such a man. But if I could serve him… just see him once a day… I’d be happy.”

Her head fell on her mother’s shoulder. Tears flowed. The mother cried too. “You must forget him,” she said. “If he doesn’t come, he’s a wicked man.”

“Don’t say that,” Fanny whispered. “He’s the best man alive. Bows says he’s unhappy. It wasn’t his fault we met. It wasn’t his fault I can’t see him. He says I must forget him—and I will. But I’ll never stop loving him. Never. I’ll pray for him. I’ll love him until I die. And when I die, my spirit will go to him.”

“You’ll break my heart,” the mother said. “But maybe he’ll come back. Maybe he’ll come today. If ever I saw a man in love, it’s him.”

At that moment, Mr. Bolton entered the lodge. The conversation stopped instantly. Mrs. Bolton smiled, patted her husband’s arm, and said, “Lor, Mr. B., who’d have thought you’d be away from the Club on a Saturday night?” Fanny wiped her tears, took up her knitting, and sat quietly in the corner—so still, so demure, that her father never suspected a thing.

Thus, fate played its cruel joke. Every person around Fanny—her mother, Bows, even the Captain—only deepened her passion. Pen wasn’t a villain. He was a gentleman. He was avoiding her because he loved her too much. And yet, that very act of restraint only made her love him more.

Was he wrong? Perhaps. Was he right? Probably. But in matters of the heart, right and wrong often look like twins separated at birth—one wearing a crown, the other a halo.

And so Fanny waited. She waited for a week. And on the seventh day, she heard from Captain Costigan that Arthur was ill—very ill.

That same evening, Arthur’s uncle, the excellent Major, arrived in town from Buxton. He had come to shoot partridges at Lord Steyne’s estate. He sent Morgan, his valet, to find Arthur and invite him to breakfast the next morning.

Morgan returned with a face like a storm cloud. “Mr. Arthur is very ill,” he reported. “In bed. Fever. Needs a doctor. I think it’s serious.”

The Major gasped. “Is he alone?”

“There’s someone nursing him,” Morgan said.

“Has he seen a doctor?”

“No, sir. He hasn’t.”

The Major sighed. “Well, I suppose I must go. I can’t leave Lord Steyne hanging. But I’ll send a doctor. And I’ll visit him tomorrow.”

And so the story continued—not with a bang, but with a cough, a fever, and a single, unspoken hope: that somewhere, in the midst of all this chaos, love might still find a way.

Fanny, poor little soul, stood in the doorway like a startled mouse caught between two cats—only these weren’t ordinary cats, they were the Pendennis women: one with eyes like dried-out raisins and a face that said “I’ve seen worse,” and the other, Laura, whose expression could freeze a summer’s day into an Arctic winter. Fanny knew instantly, just by the hollow look in Mrs. Pendennis’s eyes, that she was staring at Arthur’s mother. It was like seeing Arthur himself when he’d been tossing about in fever—same haunted glint, same kind of doom.

She looked from Mrs. Pendennis to Laura, who might as well have been carved from granite. No mercy. No sympathy. Not even a flicker of pity. Just cold, hard stone faces. Fanny turned to Major Pendennis behind them, hoping for a word of kindness, but old Pendennis only dropped his eyelids like a gentleman trying not to be seen peeking at a scandal—and then gave her a glance so furtive it could’ve been mistaken for a secret signal to a spy.

“I—I wrote to you yesterday, ma'am,” Fanny stammered, trembling like a leaf in a hurricane, pale as a sheet of paper dipped in milk. “If you please.”

“Did you, madam?” Mrs. Pendennis replied, as if Fanny had just committed a minor crime like stealing a handkerchief. “Well, I suppose I may now relieve you from nursing my son. I am his mother, you understand.”

“Yes, ma'am,” Fanny whispered, “but—this is the way to his—oh, wait a minute!” she cried, suddenly remembering something crucial. “I must prepare you for his——”

At this, Mrs. Pendennis gasped, stepped back, and let out a tiny shriek—like a cat discovering a canary in its cupboard—before quickly smothering it with a handkerchief. Too late. The damage was done.

“He’s been like this since yesterday,” Fanny said, teeth chattering like castanets in a Spanish dance.

And then, from Pen’s room—a door wide open like a stage curtain—came a sound so shocking it made the hair on your neck stand up. A horrid shriek of laughter. Then shouts. Then a full-blown college drinking song, sung with all the gusto of a man who’d just discovered gin and forgot he was dying. He thumped the wall with his fist, hollered “Hooray!” as if at a wine party, and generally behaved like a drunkard who’d just won the lottery.

“He doesn’t know me, ma'am,” Fanny said, voice cracking.

“Indeed,” Mrs. Pendennis replied, cool as ice. “Perhaps he’ll recognize his mother. Let me pass.” And with that, she pushed past Fanny like she was a doormat, marching down the dark passage toward Pen’s room. Laura sailed by too, silent as a ghost, and Major Pendennis followed, muttering something about “the state of the nation” or possibly “the state of the patient.”

Fanny sat down on a bench—formerly occupied by Pen’s printer’s devil, who’d spent many hours there waiting for ink to dry—broke down, and wept. She would have died for him. But they hated her. Not a word of thanks. Not a single “thank you, dear.” Just silence. Cold, cruel silence.

She sat there, lost in grief, until Doctor Goodenough arrived for his second visit. He found her still kneeling by the door, looking like a martyr who’d just given up hope.

“What, nurse? How’s your patient?” asked the doctor, cheerful as ever, though he knew better than anyone what a mess things were.

“Go ask them,” Fanny muttered. “They’re inside.”

“Who? His mother?”

Fanny nodded. Said nothing else.

“You must go to bed yourself, my poor little maid,” the doctor said kindly. “You’ll fall ill if you don’t.”
