“Oh, mayn’t I come and see him? Mayn’t I? I—I love him so!” she begged, falling to her knees and clutching his hand so tightly it looked like she might pull off his sleeve. The sight melted the doctor’s heart. Tears misted his spectacles. He sighed.

“Pooh, pooh! Nonsense! Nurse, has he taken his draught? Any rest? Of course you can come and see him. So can I.”

“They’ll let me sit here, won’t they, sir? I’ll never make no noise. I only ask to stay,” Fanny pleaded.

The doctor called her a “stupid little thing,” patted her cheek, and bustled into the room.

Inside, Mrs. Pendennis had already claimed her throne—literally. She sat in a great chair beside Pen’s bed, Bible on lap, watch on table, bonnet and cloak neatly placed by the window. She’d already removed Fanny’s shawl and bonnet from Pen’s drawers and flung them onto the study table like evidence of a crime. She’d locked the door on the Major and Laura and declared herself the queen of the sickroom.

She’d feared Arthur wouldn’t recognize her—but lo and behold, he did. He smiled, nodded, and began talking nonsense about being back at Fairoaks. Laura, outside, heard every word. Each laugh was a dagger to her heart. *So it’s true*, she thought. *He was guilty—and with that creature!* A servant-maid! And she’d loved him! And now he was probably dying, raving, unrepentant. The Major mumbled a few words of comfort, but Laura didn’t hear them. She was too busy imagining Arthur’s last moments—probably shouting “Hooray!” while burning in hell.

The room was dismal. Until Dr. Goodenough arrived—like an angel with a lancet.

Doctors don’t just heal the sick. They heal the *family*. Their presence alone can calm a room full of panic. We’ve all waited for that carriage to clatter down the street. That moment when the wheels stop—*thud-thud-thud*—and you know: *It’s him.* You hang on every word. A smile? Relief. A sigh? Despair. You watch the doctor’s face like a hawk watches a mouse. Is there hope? Or is it over?

Mrs. Pendennis stared into his eyes like a woman reading her own future. If there was light—she’d breathe again. If not—she’d faint.

Goodenough examined Pen, confirmed the high fever, and declared immediate action necessary. Bleeding. Now. He consoled the mother with the best news he could give: “No reason to despair yet. Youth. Constitution. Strength. All in his favor.”

Then he took the Major aside into Warrington’s bedroom (now empty, sadly), for a private consultation.

“The case is critical,” he said. “Bleeding required. Must inform the mother.”

“And there was another young lady brought with her?” the Major asked, suspiciously. “Out of place in a sickroom.”

“And there was another woman still,” the Major added, lowering his voice. “The—the little person who opened the door. The one with the bonnet and shawl. Did you notice her? I just caught a glimpse as we passed in—and begad, she was uncommonly nice-looking.”

The doctor blinked. Smiled. In the midst of life-and-death drama, humor sneaks in like a thief in the night. Sometimes it’s the only thing that keeps you from crying.

“I have it,” said the doctor, returning to the study. He scribbled two notes, sealed one, picked up Fanny’s shawl and bonnet, and went out to find her.

“Quick, nurse,” he said. “Take this to the surgeon. Tell him to come instantly. Then go to my house in Hanover Square. Ask for Harbottle. Tell him to prepare this prescription. Wait until it’s ready. It may take time.”

So Fanny trudged off, notes in hand, to the apothecary in the Strand. The man came immediately—lancet in pocket, ready to bleed the poor boy. Then Fanny ran to the doctor’s house, where Harbottle was taking forever to mix the potion. By the time it was ready, the doctor was already back.

And from that day forward, poor Fanny never returned to nurse Arthur again. But for a few days, a small, sad figure could be seen lurking near Pen’s staircase—asking the apothecary, his boy, the laundress, and the doctor himself: “Is he better? Is he sleeping? Does he know me?”

On the third day, the doctor’s carriage stopped at Shepherd’s Inn. He entered the porter’s lodge and treated a little patient—Fanny Bolton—who had been suffering quietly in the shadows. And when he finally told her the crisis was over, that Arthur was recovering, her tears turned to joy.

Mr. Costigan, Esquire, late of Her Majesty’s service, saw the doctor’s carriage and commented on the horses. “Green liveries, bedad!” he said. “And as fine a pair of high-stepping bee horses as ever a gentleman need sit behind. No end to the pride and arrogance of doctors nowadays—though this one’s a good fellow, and a scientific character. He’s brought the poor little girl through her fever, Bows, me boy!”

From that day on, whenever Mr. Costigan saw Dr. Goodenough’s carriage, he saluted it like royalty—as if the doctor were the Lord Lieutenant and Costigan himself the hero of Phoenix Park.

Mrs. Pendennis, meanwhile, was so grateful she wanted to give Goodenough the silver-gilt vase—the pride of Fairoaks, once presented to John Pendennis by Lady Elizabeth Firebrace after her son recovered from scarlet fever. The vase featured Hippocrates, Hygeia, King Bladud, and a wreath of serpents—crafted by Messrs. Abednego of Milsom Street, with an inscription by Mr. Birch, tutor to the young baronet.

She decided this treasure belonged to Goodenough. But there was one favor she refused—even though he asked for it: to treat poor Fanny kindly. The doctor had learned enough during their conversations to know Fanny was innocent—just a heartbroken girl who’d stayed by Arthur’s side out of love, not mischief. But whenever he mentioned her name, Mrs. Pendennis’s face hardened like iron. The doctor realized it was hopeless. He stopped asking.

Ah, jealousy. The one illness no medicine can cure. Not poppy, not mandragora, not mesmerism, not even Dr. Simpson’s chloroform. It’s the disease of the heart, and it runs deep in ladies.

Now, some might ask: how did all these people fit into a set of chambers in the Temple? Three rooms, two cupboards, a passage, a coal-box? Well, almost everyone was out of town. Fashionable folks fled London like rats from a sinking ship. Even Percy Sibwright, the dashing lawyer from Baker Street, had left. His bedroom—once the most elegant retreat in Lamb Court—was now available for Miss Bell (aka Mrs. Pendennis) to rest.

Sibwright’s room was a marvel: a brass bed with pink-lined chintz curtains, a mignonette box by the window, boots arranged like soldiers, a museum of scent bottles, and portraits of ladies in distress—Medora lamenting Conrad, Princesse Fleur de Marie trapped in a convent cage, Dorothea washing her feet eternally. It was a gallery of romance, fit for a poet.

And years later, Laura confessed—with a blush and a laugh—that she’d once read a French novel in that very room. When her husband asked where she’d gotten it, she admitted: “In the Temple. In Percy Sibwright’s chambers.”

“And also,” she added, “I never confessed before: I opened his japanned box, took out that strange wig, put it on, and looked at myself in the mirror.”

Imagine if Sibwright had walked in! What would he have said? The enraptured rogue! His collection of disguised beauties would’ve been nothing compared to that living, breathing, wig-wearing wonder.

But those were younger days. When people were young. When Sibwright was still a bachelor. When Laura could play pranks without fear of consequences.

When she did it, Pen couldn’t have been very ill—otherwise, decorum would’ve prevented such tomfoolery.

But events had changed everything. Martha, the faithful servant from Fairoaks, had arrived. She’d been summoned by the Major, who wisely thought her presence would be comforting. And indeed, she became a pillar of strength—sleeping on Warrington’s straw mattress among his math books, like a guardian angel.

Meanwhile, Pen’s condition improved dramatically. The fever, subdued by blisters, potions, and a good bleeding, began to recede. His mind cleared. He kissed his mother, blessed her, called for Laura and the Major, thanked them warmly, and then—after being gently ejected by his nurse—sank into a glorious sixteen-hour sleep.

When he woke, he called out: “I’m hungry!”

Oh, the joys of convalescence! To go from loathing food to craving it like a starving lion. The pleasures fade with age, alas. But for now, it was pure bliss.

On that happy day, another arrival came—Warrington, fresh from Norfolk, having rushed back upon hearing of Arthur’s plight. He burst into the chambers with a cigar in his mouth and a carpet-bag under his arm, puffing smoke like a steam engine.

He found the Major sitting demurely in an easy chair, listening to a young lady reading Shakespeare aloud—softly, sweetly.

She stopped. Started. Put down her book.

Warrington blushed. Threw the cigar into the passage. Took off his hat. Dropped it. Rushed to the Major, seized his hand, and asked about Arthur.

The Major answered, voice trembling but cheerful. He told him of the crisis, the mother’s arrival, the young charge—Miss—

“You need not tell me her name,” Warrington said, eyes bright with emotion. “I know it. It’s Laura.”

And he reached out, took her hand.

Tenderness shone from under his rough eyebrows. “Arthur’s hero,” his eyes seemed to say. “The brave one. The kind one. Who traveled hundreds of miles to help his friend.”

“Thank you, Mr. Warrington,” Laura said simply, her face flushing crimson. She was glad the lamp was behind her.

As they stood there, the door to Pen’s room opened softly—just as Mrs. Pendennis always did—and Warrington saw another lady, who looked at him, then turned toward the bed, and whispered: “Hsh!” with a raised hand.

And so the scene remained—quiet, hopeful, full of secrets and tender hearts—while Arthur slept, and the world held its breath.

Now, let us record a fact concerning Arthur Pendennis—so shocking, so mortifying, that if it were told of any other hero in fiction, the reader would instantly suspect a plot or a hoax. But since this is the true and unvarnished memoir of our dear Pen, we must lay bare the truth: after suffering from fever and the tender affliction of love (a most inconvenient combination), he recovered—not just his health, but his sense.

Yes, indeed. After being bled, blistered, shaved like a monk, and dosed with every known remedy under the sun (and some not yet invented), the young man rose from his sickbed—and found himself utterly cured of his passion for Fanny Bolton. Not merely cooled off, mind you—completely over her. As if she had never existed. As if he had never lain awake at night imagining her eyes, her voice, her hand upon his brow during delirium (which may have been a dream, or possibly a hallucination involving a pigeon). He now looked back on his former infatuation with the same pity one might feel for a man who once believed in phrenology or believed he saw a ghost in the gaslight.

He laughed—gently, as one laughs when recovering from a fever dream—as he lay propped up on pillows, contemplating how absurd it was to have loved Fanny Bolton. She was nice enough, certainly. Pleasant company. But not exactly a goddess. No wit, no charm, no beauty to speak of—unless one counted her ability to keep a kettle warm and her skill in mending socks. And yet! There he’d been, burning with desire, heart pounding, letters written and then torn up, all because of her. It wasn’t her fault. It was him. The illness had burned out the flame. His own internal furnace had gone cold. The fever took the fire with it, along with the headache, the nausea, and perhaps even the last vestiges of his dignity.

And what comfort it gave him—this selfish comfort, which we all know well—that he hadn’t given in. That he hadn’t done anything rash or improper while half-mad with sickness. That he could look back and say, “I resisted.” Like a man escaping a pit of vipers by falling into a tub of lukewarm soup. He felt proud—though ashamed of feeling proud. Because, really, isn’t it humiliating to admit you no longer love someone? It’s almost as bad as admitting you’ve stopped believing in fairies.

Meanwhile, the presence of his mother at his bedside was a soothing balm. Her gentle smiles, her tireless care, her constant offer of tea and toast—these things filled the room with peace. To see him getting better was all she asked for. To fulfill his whims—whether it be a cold compress, a book read aloud, or a request for a slice of cake at midnight—was her joy. He felt wrapped in maternal warmth, as though he were five again, and she was still scolding him for leaving his toys on the floor.

But here comes the rub. Pen had vague memories—foggy, half-formed dreams—of Fanny Bolton tending him. Was she really there? Or was that just a fever-induced fantasy where she appeared as a nurse, a saint, a vision in blue cotton? He couldn’t tell. And since he’d never mentioned Fanny to his mother before, he couldn’t bring it up now. So silence reigned—on both sides. A silence that, in time, would cause more pain than any confession ever could.

Mrs. Pendennis, seeing Fanny Bolton installed as nurse, naturally drew conclusions. And what conclusions? That Arthur had been unfaithful. That the rumors were true. That her son, whom she adored, had broken every rule of decency. Why didn’t she ask? Because women often believe the worst about men they love. Especially their sons. Isn’t it true that wives are usually the first to accuse their husbands of infidelity? Poor Pen got a double dose of that kind of love—the kind that watches too closely and suspects too much. His mother thought he’d not only fallen ill, but also fallen into sin. That he was weakened by disease *and* stained by moral failure. And so she bore her sorrow silently, masking her despair with cheerful smiles and endless cups of tea.

Meanwhile, across the Channel, Captain Shandon, lounging in Boulogne with a glass of brandy (two francs a bottle—what a bargain!), read the latest issue of the *Pall Mall Gazette*. “Jack Finucane’s hand is gone,” he remarked to Mrs. Shandon. “Warrington’s back.”

“Oh, Charles!” said Mrs. Shandon, “how can you talk like that? Mr. Warrington is such a kind gentleman. He was so good to the children.”

“Kind to children,” replied Shandon, “but savage with men. You don’t understand writing, my dear. It’s like hunting. Warrington doesn’t slash—he dissects. He cuts straight down the spine. One line bleeds. Another finishes the job. I call it elegant slaughter.”

“Good heavens, Charles!”

“Ah, but it’s better here. Wine flows freely. We’re happy. Mix another tumbler, Mary. Soon we’ll return to the fray. ‘Cras ingens iterabimus aequor’—bad luck to it.”

Back in London, Warrington had taken over Pen’s duties at the *Gazette* with gusto. He wrote articles, reviews, critiques of plays and concerts—all with the force of a bull charging through a china shop. Yet when he spoke of Arthur, he became oddly tender.

“The people here don’t understand style,” he told Mrs. Pendennis. “They wouldn’t recognize genius if it bit them. But Arthur has it. He’s graceful. Light. Elegant. I call him mine—I raised him. I’m proud of him. Yes, he’s wilful, selfish, dandyish—but honest, loyal, gentle. His pen can be cruel, but his heart is soft as a lady’s glove. I swear he’d never harm a fly.”

At this, Helen sighed deeply. Laura blushed. But both were grateful. They loved Warrington for loving Pen.

Major Pendennis, meanwhile, was positively ecstatic. “He’s a gentleman,” he declared, “every inch. Suffolk Warringtons. Barons. Sir Miles Warrington—ran away with—oh, beg your pardon, Miss Bell.” He paused, embarrassed. “Sir Miles was a great man. Friend to the Prince of Wales. This man? Brilliant. Talented. Sure to rise—if he had a motive.”

Laura blushed again—this time not for shame, but for something else. For she had begun to wonder about Warrington. About his dark eyes, his quiet strength, his melancholy smile. Had he been hurt? By love? By life? She wondered, and blushed.

Warrington took rooms nearby—Grenier’s chambers in Flag Court. In the mornings, he worked hard. In the afternoons, he came to visit Pen. And sometimes, when Laura asked permission to walk in the Temple Gardens with him, Major Pendennis cried, “Of course! Everyone walks there. Beadles, police, ladies, gentlemen—it’s like the country!”

So why not? And so they walked. And talked. And laughed. And Warrington, who had never met a woman like Laura—pure, intelligent, untouched by city gossip—began to see her differently. And she, in turn, found him fascinating—raw, real, full of energy and honesty, unlike Pen, who seemed to glide through life like a bored prince, indifferent to everything but fashion and flirtation.

What made Pen such a dandy? The women, of course. They spoiled him. Praised him. Obedied him. Until he grew tired of flattery, like a king tired of courtiers. Abroad, he was lively, passionate—because abroad, no one bowed to him. At home, he yawned. He was used to being worshipped. And when worship becomes routine, it loses its charm.

But Laura? She loved him anyway. Why? Because she saw little else at Fairoaks except Doctor Portman and Captain Glanders (the latter of whom, rumor had it, kept a pet raccoon). Because her mother praised Arthur constantly. Because he was handsome, witty, and noble. And because, above all, she was built to love someone. It’s human nature. You meet someone. You hear them praised. You sit beside them in church. You ride together. You fall in love. It’s not rational. It’s instinct. Like hunger. Like thirst. You don’t fall in love because the person is perfect—you fall in love because they’re *there*.

And so, when she came to London and met Warrington—strange, serious, brilliant—she found him odd, original, and rather delightful. Who could blame her?

Years later, when fate had scattered them all—some married, some dead, some forgotten—they might have looked back and remembered those evenings in Lamb Court: the music, the laughter, the games of ecarte, the sherry brought by the Marquis of Steyne’s Amontillado (a wine so precious it could make a man weep), the piano played by Laura, the clock chimes of St. Clement’s ringing out at ten o’clock, the slippers knitted in worsted yarn, and the Damascus scimitar hanging in Sibwright’s room, ready to defend the honor of the household against any intruder—even a particularly persistent mouse.

One evening, as the chimes tolled, Laura folded her knitting. Martha from Fairoaks arrived with a candle. The Major sighed. “God bless my soul, is it so late?” Warrington stood. They shook hands. Martha lighted them down the stairs. And as they descended, she bolted the door behind them—“that thar hooky soord” ready, should danger come.

And outside, in the quiet dusk, the pale girl in the black bonnet stood near the lamp-post, listening to the music fade. Perhaps she smiled. Perhaps she sighed. Perhaps she wished she could stay. But no. She was led away by an old gentleman, probably her father, who clearly thought her too young to be out after dark.

And so ended another day in the life of Arthur Pendennis—convalescent, cured, somewhat wiser, and already forgetting the very thing that had once consumed him.

Good night, Laura. Good night, Fanny. Good night, Warrington. Good night, Pendennis. Sleep well. And tomorrow—well, tomorrow is another story.

CHAPTER LV. Fanny’s Occupation’s Gone

Ever since her son’s illness, good Mrs. Pendennis—Helen, as we’ve come to know her—had taken over Arthur’s entire life like a well-meaning but slightly overzealous housekeeper. His drawers, his closets, even the very socks and shirts that needed mending (and there were many) had all fallen under her watchful eye. And then, of course, there were the letters—those mysterious, paper-wrapped confessions that had piled up in his chambers while he lay weak and unconscious. Letters from bootmakers demanding payment, hatters with urgent bills, and, more troublingly, one or two from a certain Miss Fanny Bolton—whose name, though never spoken aloud, hung over the household like a cloud.

Mrs. Pendennis, bless her, was not cruel; she simply couldn’t bear the thought of her boy being led astray by such a girl. She’d removed the brass knocker from the inner door of Arthur’s room so that the postman’s knock wouldn’t disturb him—though it also meant no one could deliver any mail without her permission. It was a small act of maternal control, but one that carried great weight. She read every letter herself, answered them when necessary, and kept the whole correspondence under lock and key—especially those from Fanny Bolton.

And why not? Pen, as Arthur was affectionately known, had always been a free spirit—a man who gave generously, laughed loudly, and paid little attention to bills or consequences. But this made his mother all the more anxious. She had saved money—carefully, frugally, almost stingily—and now she used it to settle his debts. Not because he asked, but because she believed in honor, in duty, in the idea that a gentleman should never be burdened by shame. At least, that’s what she told herself.

She didn’t tell him about Fanny. Not yet. But she did ask her brother-in-law, Major Pendennis, whether he knew anything about “that dreadful affair.” The Major, a man whose morals were shaped more by experience than by doctrine, sat on a bench at Richmond with a look of profound amusement, as if he’d just heard a joke too good to spoil.

“Ah,” said the Major, squinting through his spectacles. “You want to know about Fanny Bolton?”

“I do,” Helen replied, clutching her handkerchief. “I must know. My son—my dear Arthur—is involved in some terrible scandal.”

The Major sighed, leaned back, and said: “My dear, I don’t know anything about it. And I don’t wish to know. And if you’re asking for my advice, I suggest you don’t know either.”

“You don’t think it serious?” Helen asked, trembling.

“Serious?” said the Major, raising an eyebrow. “Is it worse than flirting with a grisette? Because if so, I’d say you’re overreacting. Young men will be young men. If every mother broke her heart over every little flirtation—why, half England would be mourning.”

Helen gasped. “But it’s not just flirting! It’s a sin! A crime!”

“Oh, come now,” said the Major, gently. “What proof have you? Has Arthur confessed? Has anyone accused him? Or is it just a letter?”

“A letter,” she whispered. “Only one. From her. I found it. I opened it.”

“Did you?” said the Major, pretending surprise. “And what did it say?”

“It was written in bad spelling,” Helen said, “and full of sorrow. She said she was unhappy. That her father was cruel. That she loved him. That she prayed for his health. And—oh, Major, I can’t bear it—she signed it ‘Your poor little Fanny.’”

The Major chuckled. “Well, I’ll be damned. That sounds like a love letter. Not a blackmail note. Not a declaration of war. Just a girl writing to the boy who once took her to Vauxhall Gardens and paid for her ice cream.”

“But she was in his rooms!” Helen insisted. “While he was ill!”

“Yes,” said the Major, “but only after he was delirious. He didn’t know who she was. And Flanagan, the laundress, says she came with Mr. Bows, that old Irish gentleman who helped me get away from Stillbrook—by the way, I never paid the cab fare. Quite forgettable, really.”

“And the girl’s parents?” Helen pressed.

“They’re divided,” said the Major. “Her father beats her mother, who insists the girl is innocent. But the mother also told Goodenough—our dear doctor—that Arthur treated her daughter like a brute. So, you see, the story is murky. But here’s the thing: if Arthur had done something truly awful, wouldn’t they have come forward? Wouldn’t the girl’s family have demanded justice? Instead, they’re keeping quiet. And Arthur hasn’t said a word. So perhaps—just perhaps—he’s innocent.”

“Innocent?” Helen repeated, tears glistening. “Oh, thank God! Thank Heaven!”

The Major patted her hand. “Now, listen. If your son has done nothing wrong, why punish him with suspicion? If he has done something… well, then he’ll confess. But until then, let’s not assume guilt. Let’s not make him feel like a criminal before he’s even committed a crime.”

Helen wiped her eyes and stood up. “I’ll go to him now. I’ll show him the letter. I’ll ask him plainly. He’s innocent. I know he is.”

“Don’t,” said the Major, stopping her. “If you confront him, and he denies it, you’ll break his trust. If he admits it, you’ll break his heart. And if he’s guilty—well, then you’ll have to marry him off to Fanny Bolton. Which, by Jove, would be quite a scene.”

Helen laughed, despite herself. “Marry Fanny Bolton?”

“Or worse—marry Mrs. Flanagan,” the Major added, grinning. “She’s a fine woman, but she drinks gin. And she’s already offered to take care of Arthur. I wouldn’t mind. But I suspect she’d expect a ring.”

Helen giggled. “You’re terrible, brother.”

“I’m practical,” said the Major. “And I’ve seen enough of life to know that sometimes the best way to avoid a scandal is to ignore it. Like a dog ignoring a bone it doesn’t want.”

So, instead of confrontation, they decided on a plan. They would travel abroad. To the Rhine. To a watering-place where no one knew Arthur’s name, where the air was fresh, and the memories faded. Doctor Goodenough agreed—it was the best cure for both mother and son. And Warrington, Arthur’s closest friend, was invited along.

But Warrington hesitated. “I can’t go,” he said. “I’ve got responsibilities. And no money.”

Arthur looked crushed. “But you must come! You’re part of the family now.”

“No,” said Warrington. “I’ve got to stay. I’ve got to earn a living.”

And so the party dwindled. But Arthur, ever hopeful, turned to his mother. “We’ll go anyway. We’ll leave tomorrow. I’ll write to Warrington later. He’ll understand.”

And indeed, as the sun set over Richmond, the mood lightened. The fear of Fanny Bolton faded into the distance. The letter was tucked away. The past was buried. And for the first time in weeks, Arthur felt like himself again.

Later, as he walked with Warrington on the terrace, he confessed: “You know, I was tempted. Very tempted. But I escaped. Thanks to my illness.”

Warrington nodded gravely. “It’s easier to run than to fight. Especially when the enemy is a girl with pretty eyes and a broken heart.”

Arthur smiled. “Exactly. And the best thing about running? You don’t have to explain yourself afterward.”

They laughed. And somewhere, far away, Fanny Bolton probably wondered what had become of the man who once promised her the moon.

But that, of course, was another story.

CHAPTER LVI. In Which Fanny Engages a New Medical Man (With a Side of Whisky and Regret)

Could Helen have guessed that as Pen’s strength returned, so too would his old-fashioned crush on little Fanny? Not likely. She never said a word about the girl after her talk with the Major—she didn’t even glance in her direction. Yet Mrs. Pendennis watched Arthur like a hawk under a microscope, citing “ill health” as her excuse to keep him tethered to her side like a particularly fragile pet. She forbade all correspondence—“For now,” she declared, though we know full well she meant “forever.” Poor Arthur must’ve trembled when letters arrived at the table. He’d feel her gaze—though it was fixed firmly on her teacup or the edge of a novel—and he’d expect every day to see *that* handwriting: tiny, looping, unmistakable, even if he’d never actually seen it before. His heart would thud like a drumroll whenever the mail came. Was he relieved or disappointed when no letter materialized? Did he secretly hope for a cry from Fanny? “Help, Arthur!” Or did he wish she’d just vanish into the ether?

Ah, love! When Lovelace tires of Clarissa—or vice versa—it’s best to split clean, like a bad marriage in a Dickens novel. But human nature hates abrupt bankruptcies. We don’t want to put up the “Closed” sign overnight. We prefer a slow decline: mournful board meetings, half-hearted reconciliations, pawned jewelry to keep appearances. So Pen, despite his recovery, found himself annoyed by Fanny’s silence. Could she really leave without so much as a backward glance? Could she sink beneath the waves without throwing out a single hand? Some drown. Most just get dunked and scrambles to shore. And based on our experience with A. Pendennis, Esq., of the Upper Temple, we can say with confidence: this man was more likely to be drenched than drowned.

Pen was still too weak to walk half a mile—no sirree. He couldn’t even take a carriage ride without a nurse clinging to his arm like a limpet. But poor Helen couldn’t watch over Mr. Warrington. That gentleman had business in London, and who was she to stop him? If anything, she might’ve been glad to see him go—except conscience intervened. She remembered Warrington’s loyalty, his kindness to her boy, and so welcomed him into their home with her usual melancholy grace. Still, one morning, she somehow knew why Warrington had gone to town: to find out what had become of Fanny.

Arthur had indeed confided in Warrington, telling him everything—the whole sordid, romantic mess with Fanny, which the reader already knows better than the characters themselves. He expressed gratitude for escaping disaster—Warrington agreed wholeheartedly. Pen admitted he had no great sins to confess regarding Fanny; but if they were to part, he’d be glad to say, “God bless you,” and hope she’d remember him kindly. He spoke with such solemnity, such emotion, that George Warrington—who had previously championed the breakup—began to worry. Was Arthur truly cured? Would a reunion bring back all the temptation, all the danger?

“It’s hard to resist, Arthur,” Warrington said, “and easy to fall. The bravest thing we poor wretches can do is flee danger. I wouldn’t be where I am today if I’d done what I preach.”

“And what did you do, George?” Pen asked eagerly. “I knew there was something. Tell me.”

“There was something,” Warrington replied, “that shattered my life early. I said I’d tell you someday. Not now. Take the moral without the fable. Here I am—wrecked by a rock I hit as a boy. So beware.”

Meanwhile, young Mr. Huxter, in his letter home to Clavering, mentioned a fashionable club in London where he met an Irish officer of distinction—none other than Captain Costigan. This club was the Back Kitchen, a nightly haunt for medical students and gentlemen of questionable sobriety. Huxter, a natural mimic, delighted in imitating the General’s brogue, mannerisms, and drunken ramblings. With sixpence worth of brandy-and-water, Costigan would open up like a book—telling tales of his daughter’s triumphs, his military exploits, and his profound sorrow over Emily’s ingratitude. “My grey hairs are hastening to a premature grave,” he’d lament, eyes glistening with whiskey tears.

The boys at the Back Kitchen began to wonder: Where did this mysterious General live? Was it a watchbox? A cab at a mews? The Duke of York’s Column? Huxter, ever the fantasist, spread wild rumors. But truth was simpler: Costigan lived in Shepherd’s Inn, in a garret above the porter’s lodge—though he never revealed it, not because of shame, but because privacy had long been his armor.

One bright September morning, Huxter, fresh from Vauxhall and fueled by coffee, spotted the General reeling down Henrietta Street, trailed by a pack of giggling urchins. The old man was barely conscious, eyes glazed, staggering like a drunkard on a sinking ship. Cabmen muttered, policemen scowled, but Costigan didn’t care. He followed his usual route home—until a rogue orange peel tripped him.

Huxter sprang forward. After a moment of dazed confusion, he helped the General up and insisted on escorting him. “You’re not going anywhere alone,” he said cheerfully. The General protested, claiming he could manage, but he lurched and swayed like a drunken puppet. Huxter guided him across the street, past the ancient gate of Shepherd’s Inn, where the ornate coat of arms seemed to sneer at the chaos unfolding.

Old Mr. Bolton, the porter, emerged grumbling. “Don’t bother me,” he snapped. “Go to your own bed, Captain. Don’t keep honest men awake.” Costigan staggered inside, and Huxter followed, helping him up the stairs, unlocking the door with the key Costigan kept in his pocket. Once inside, Huxter assisted the old man undress, checked for broken bones (none), and applied compresses to his scraped knee and shin—both torn through his trousers during the fall.

At Costigan’s age, wounds healed slowly. Fever set in. The General lay ill for days, moaning and sweating.

Huxter took charge of the case with gusto. He visited daily, offering lively conversation and medicine. He instructed the nurse on whisky rations—strictly limited, since the patient couldn’t move. Bows, Mrs. Bolton, and little Fanny took turns tending the sick man. Huxter became a fixture in the inn, friendly with everyone from the porter to the garret dwellers.

He thought he’d seen Fanny somewhere before—but couldn’t place her. Of course not. She hadn’t told him where they’d met. He’d seen her when he was half-drunk, dancing at Vauxhall. And she herself had changed—pale, thin, haunted by three weeks of fever, despair, and unrequited longing. Her face was wan, her eyes often fixed on the postman’s back as he passed. Many a tear fell into the General’s gruel.

Ah, poor little Fanny! Stir the gruel well, and have courage. If every soul who suffered like you died instantly, the undertakers would make a fortune.

Huxter, charmed by the General’s plight, visited two or three times a day. He brought toys for the children, apples, brandy-balls, even a mask to frighten them. He made Fanny smile once—just once. He called Mrs. Bolton “Mrs. B.” and teased her freely, unlike “that aughty, artless beast” whom she now detested—namely, Arthur Pendennis.

From Mrs. Bolton, Huxter learned the truth about Fanny’s illness—and Pen’s behavior. Naturally, Mrs. Bolton’s version was biased. She painted Arthur as a villain: seducer, liar, breaker of promises, worthy of public scorn. Huxter, still smarting from Pen’s earlier snubs, believed every word.

But why didn’t he write to Clavering? Why not expose the young man’s misconduct?

He did write—only to say that Pendennis had narrowly escaped death, and that Clavering would rejoice. He mentioned his interesting case: a distinguished officer with a compound fracture. But Fanny? Not a word. Just like Pen himself.

O mothers of England! How much do you *really* know about your sons?

Bows, however, got the full story. Huxter confided in him, calling Pen a “confounded conceited blackguard” and vowing to punch him in the nose when he recovered.

Then Bows spoke. He told his version: how Pen had stumbled upon Fanny by accident—not design—how he’d acted with honor, how Mrs. Bolton was a fool. He recounted the conversation between himself and Pen, the young man’s noble sentiments.

Huxter felt ashamed. He admitted he’d been wrong. No punch was thrown.

But Huxter’s kindness toward Fanny didn’t go unnoticed. Bows, ever jealous, saw it as a betrayal. “I only have to like someone,” he muttered, “and someone else comes along and takes my place. It’s always been so—since I was a lad. What can a man like me expect? To be laughed at? The young succeed. The old fail. I’ve played second fiddle all my life. Why should luck change now?”

Fanny, meanwhile, received Huxter’s attentions with quiet gratitude. She laughed at his jokes, played games with his little sisters. But her sadness returned quickly—so swiftly that Bows should’ve known Huxter wasn’t winning her heart. But Bows didn’t see. He misread everything.

Fanny blamed Bows for Pen’s silence. She hated him. She turned from him when he spoke. She rejected his comfort. Poor Bows—his devotion went unrewarded, his heart unheeded.

When Warrington arrived at Shepherd’s Inn, he sought Bows’s rooms—no doubt per prior arrangement. He didn’t glimpse Fanny. He was directed to the musician’s chamber, where Bows greeted him warmly. They shook hands. After small talk, Warrington said he came from Arthur Pendennis and his family—to thank Bows for his help during Pen’s illness, and for fetching the Major.

Bows replied humbly: “It was my duty. I never thought I’d see him alive again. I’m glad he’s recovering.”

“Lucky are those who have friends,” said Bows. “I might be up here, and nobody would care whether I lived or died.”

“What about the General?” Warrington asked.

“The General likes his whisky more than anything,” Bows said. “We live together out of habit. He doesn’t care for me any more than you do. Now, what’s this about? You’re not here to visit me. Nobody visits me. It’s about Fanny, isn’t it?”

“Yes,” Warrington said.

“Does Mr. Pendennis want to see her now that he’s better? Does his lordship propose to throw his handkerchief to her? She’s been very ill—ever since Mrs. Pendennis threw her out. Kind of a lady, wasn’t it? The poor girl and I found him raving in a fever—nobody to tend him but his drunken laundress. She watched night and day. I ran to fetch his uncle. Mamma arrives and kicks Fanny out. Uncle arrives and leaves me to pay the cab. Carry my compliments to the ladies and gentlemen. Say we’re both very thankful. Why, a countess couldn’t have behaved better. And for an apothecary’s wife—well, I suppose she deserves a double-gilt pestle and mortar on her coach.”

And so, the chapter ends—with a touch of irony, a dash of regret, and a new doctor who may just be the cure Fanny needs.

Worthy Major Pendennis, ever the gentleman of conscience and occasional moral compass, fulfilled his promise to Warrington—just enough to satisfy his own良知, which is always a very lenient judge when it comes to family matters. He reassured poor Helen that Arthur’s connection with the odious little gatekeeper (a position she had long suspected was more of a romantic entanglement than a janitorial one) was officially over. No more secret meetings! No more clandestine glances across the garden! The romance was dead—killed by virtue, propriety, and probably a well-timed cough from the butler.

Arthur, meanwhile, once he’d recovered from the shock of realizing Fanny Bolton wasn’t going to die dramatically in his arms (a fate he’d secretly hoped for, if only for the dramatic flair), felt a great weight lifted. He could now enjoy life without fearing an impromptu proposal or a sudden marriage certificate being thrust into his hands by a distraught young woman. Freedom! At last!

And so, with hearts light and wallets slightly lighter (though not too much—Pendennis was no fool), the party set off on their grand Continental tour. Arthur Pendennis, rentier extraordinaire, accompanied Madame Pendennis and Mademoiselle Bell (who, though technically not French, did her best to act like one, speaking English with exaggerated elegance and occasionally referring to “the charming little village” when they were clearly in a town with a population of 10,000). George Warrington, particulier, age 32, height 6 feet (Anglo-Saxon standard), hair black, beard idem, etc., took charge of logistics—passports, baggage, and making sure no one got lost in Bruges while trying to find the nearest café serving coffee that wasn’t made from boiled shoe polish.

They crossed from Dover to Ostend, where they paused briefly to admire the sea, the wind, and the fact that they were finally out of England. Then onward they went—Bruges, Ghent, Brussels, and eventually the Rhine, where the scenery was so picturesque it made you want to write poetry… or at least take a photograph. But since photography hadn’t been invented yet, they settled for sighing dramatically and pretending they were characters in a novel.

Now, we don’t intend to bore you with every detail of this thoroughly unoriginal tour. We won’t describe Laura’s first encounter with a canal boat (she nearly fainted when she saw how low it sat in the water). We won’t dwell on Helen’s fascination with Beguine convents—where she spent hours staring at nuns in black veils, arms outstretched like they were auditioning for a particularly solemn ballet. She found the whole thing deeply unsettling. Barefoot friars? Crowned saints? People bowing down to images of the Virgin Mary like they were auditioning for a religious reality show? It was all so *foreign*. And then there were the theatres open on Sundays—blasphemy! The idea of dancing on a holy day? Unthinkable! Yet here they were, doing it, and looking rather pleased about it.

When the men returned from their evening walks or drives, they’d find Helen quietly reading her devotional books—psalms, hymns, and other things that didn’t involve modernity. Laura would watch her, eyes full of concern, wondering if her mother-in-law-to-be (if things ever came to that) was holding up under the strain of foreign customs and emotional turmoil. Poor Pen, meanwhile, was devotedly attentive to his mother, though there was something missing—a kind of invisible wall between them. She loved him fiercely, but also felt the sting of betrayal: once, her son had poured out his soul to her; now, he kept secrets. She missed those days when Arthur’s heart was hers alone—when he’d tell her everything, even what color socks he thought looked best with his waistcoat.

Laura, observing this quiet agony, once said softly, “If Pen had loved me as you wished, I’d have gained him—but I’d have lost you, mamma. And I’d rather have you love me best.” To which Helen sighed and agreed—though she firmly denied the part about Pen loving Laura. But let’s be honest: Miss Laura was probably right on both counts. Women, after all, are built for love—not just in moments, but in hours, days, years. Men? They’re more like oaks. Standing tall. Hands in pockets. Occasionally offering shade. Women? They’re the ivy. Clinging. Twining. Kissing the bark. Devoting themselves entirely to the tree—even if it doesn’t know it’s being worshipped.

Meanwhile, old Pendennis had only seen them off at the dock before vanishing into the mist of European diplomacy. He was off to visit a nobleman friend—someone important, someone whose name appeared in the Morning Post alongside “Marquis of Steyne,” which was quite a thrill for the Major. He’d earned a break after years of tending to sick family members, though he did admit he missed the partridges at Stillbrook. Still, the pheasants were still around, and so was the noble owner, so he headed straight to the hospitable mansion and began enjoying himself—shooting, dining, and generally behaving like a man who’d forgotten he was nearing seventy.

He was a treasure in a country house. He told risqué stories in the smoking room (which the young men laughed at, mostly because they didn’t understand half of them). He flattered the ladies in the morning rooms. He showed new arrivals around the park, pointing out the best view of the lake, the spot where the old road used to go, and the exact location where Lord Lynx caught Sir Phelim O’Neal praying on his knees before the lady of the house—*very* dramatic, though possibly apocryphal. He knew everyone’s names—lodge-keepers, gardeners, the cook’s nephew—and had a word for each, usually something mildly critical. He was invaluable. And he enjoyed every minute of it.

Back on the Continent, Warrington became the de facto leader of the expedition. He spoke German fluently (unlike Pen, who had forgotten most of it during his illness). He handled money, managed luggage, and ensured the group never ended up in a hotel with no hot water. He found the English church, walked with Helen and Laura to services (with proper decorum, naturally), and even escorted the widow on her donkey rides—though he once almost fell off when the animal startled at a passing goat.

At the baths, many a dashing Prussian or French dandy cast longing glances at Laura, the fresh-faced English girl beside the pale widow. They’d have given anything to dance with her—except, of course, Warrington, who had never learned to waltz. Not that he minded. He’d have liked to learn—if only to dance with *her*. But alas, such thoughts were forbidden territory. He was a bachelor, after all. A rough diamond with no intention of becoming a polished gem. Or so he told himself.

Yet he stayed. Day after day, he watched over Helen, attended to her needs, fetched her ‘Galignani’ (a newspaper she read with intense interest), and arranged comfortable seats under lime trees during the afternoon promenades. He was so attentive, in fact, that people began to wonder if he was actually her son. Or perhaps an adventurer hoping to marry her fortune. Maybe both. After all, this was a *selfish story*, remember? Everyone has their reasons. Even the generous ones.

Laura, meanwhile, grew more fond of Warrington with each passing day. She greeted him with bright smiles and eager conversation. He found himself talking to her in ways he never thought possible—sharing thoughts, feelings, even jokes. He started noticing things: crow’s feet near his eyes, silver hairs in his beard, the way his boots pinched his toes. He envied the young bucks—the Germans with their tight waists, the Frenchmen with their lacquered mustaches, the English dandies with their cool superiority. He felt old. Outdated. But still… he couldn’t leave.

Every night, he left the group with growing reluctance. Alone in his lodgings, he felt lonelier than ever. And Helen? She saw it. She saw the way he looked at her, the care he took with her, the quiet devotion. She understood why Major Pendennis had insisted Warrington join them—because he, too, was watching. Watching over her. Watching over her son. Watching over *everything*.

Pen, however, remained blissfully unaware. He didn’t see what was happening. Didn’t care. Or maybe he did—but chose not to interfere. After all, he had his own concerns: his health, his mother’s fragile state, and the lingering guilt over Fanny Bolton.

Which brings us to the moment when a letter arrived from London. Delivered by Morgan, the Major’s valet, who carried it like it was a sacred relic. Inside was a note from Fanny Bolton—written in trembling hand, filled with sorrow and devotion.

She’d come to his rooms, she said. Found them empty. Left no message. And when she’d written to him, asking only for one kind word, her letter had vanished. She’d cared for him when he was ill, cut off his hair (a shocking intimacy!), and kept a lock of it—just a small token. Could she keep it? Would his mother demand it back?

Arthur read the letter and turned red. Then white. Then red again. He stormed into the Major’s room, letter clutched in hand.

“Do you know anything about this?” he demanded.

The Major, fresh from his bath and smelling faintly of eucalyptus and regret, blinked. “What letter?”

“The one from Fanny Bolton!”

“I know nothing about it,” the Major said sharply. “I’ve never met the girl. Never spoken to her. Never sent her away.”

“Then it was my mother!” Arthur shouted. “She did it!”

“Perhaps,” said the Major, shrugging. “But let’s not argue. I’ve got a napoleon to lose at the casino later.”

Arthur didn’t listen. He stormed out, hat in hand, muttering about justice and dignity and how his mother had treated a poor, innocent girl like dirt.

Back at the lodging, he found Warrington waiting for the ladies to attend church. He threw the letter across the table.

“Look at this,” he said. “She saved me. She loved me. And they treated her like trash.”

Warrington frowned. “If your mother did it…”

“It makes it worse,” Arthur snapped. “She should have protected her. Not rejected her.”

“Hush,” Warrington whispered. “They can hear you.”

But it was too late. The storm had begun. And somewhere, deep in the heart of Rosenbad, a tiny spark had ignited—leading, inevitably, to disaster.

CHAPTER LVIII. “Fairoaks to Let” – A Tale of Tears, Tension, and a Tiny Trust Fund

Our poor widow—poor in fortune, but rich in Martha’s loyal presence (who, bless her, giggled at German customs like they were a new kind of pickle)—had thrown a little dinner party to celebrate Major Pendennis’s arrival. But the feast was more of a funeral banquet: only the Major and two young men actually ate. Helen, the widow herself, had sent word she was too ill to join, so Laura stayed by her side like a devoted nursemaid with better posture.

The Major, ever the life of the party, chattered away as if nothing could possibly go wrong. Meanwhile, Arthur Pendennis—our hero, or at least the man who’d recently discovered he might be one—sat there feeling like his soul had been turned inside out by a very unkind tailor.

It wasn’t until evening that Helen and Laura appeared in the sitting room. Helen came in leaning on Laura, backlit by the dying sun, so Arthur couldn’t see just how ghastly pale she looked. She walked straight up to Pen, kissed him tenderly, and clung to him like a drowning woman finding a lifebuoy made of silk handkerchiefs. But Arthur didn’t return the kiss. Instead, he stared at her with the look of someone who’d just been told he’d accidentally inherited a goat.

“Mother,” he said, voice tight, “you’re looking dreadful.”

She trembled. Her fingers, cold as winter carrots, gripped his hand like a vice. “I’ve had much to annoy me,” he added, chest thumping like a drum in a bad marching band.

Helen nearly fainted. Not from emotion—no, that would have been dramatic—but because she suddenly realized Arthur might actually mean it when he said he’d marry Fanny Bolton, the kitchen maid whose heart had been broken by her own mother’s meddling.

Warrington, Laura, and the Major all held their breath. The storm was coming. And not the kind you get after a thunderstorm—you know, the *after* part where everyone feels guilty about the lightning.

Arthur continued, voice rising like a kettle left on too long: “I’ve received letters from London. One has caused me more pain than I’ve ever known. My old letters were stolen—my private thoughts, my confessions! And the girl who cared for me—loved me—has been treated cruelly… by *you*, mother!”

“Stop!” cried Warrington, leaping forward like a man who’d just seen a squirrel fall into a bucket of tea. “She’s ill! Can’t you see?”

“Let him speak,” whispered Helen, weak as a wet napkin.

“Let him kill her,” snapped Laura, rushing to her mother’s side. “Go on, sir. See her die.”

And so Arthur did. He ranted. He raged. He accused his mother of being a tyrant, a gossip, a social snob who trampled on love like it was a muddy bootprint. “You despise those who love me,” he thundered, “because they’re lower class! Well, I’ll show you! I’ll go back to Fanny Bolton and ask her to live with me. I’ll defy your pride, your suspicion, your *entire outdated worldview!*”

The Major, who had spent most of his life trying to keep Arthur from doing anything remotely noble, now stood up like a lion who’d just been insulted by a bad joke.

“By Gad!” he roared, face purple. “If you want to disgrace the name of Pendennis by marrying a kitchen wench, then go ahead! But don’t expect me to stand by. I wash my hands of you, sir! I’m an old man—don’t think I won’t leave this world soon enough. I come from a family as ancient and noble as any in England—and I thought, before I kicked the bucket, that the boy I raised would do something to honor our name. But no! You choose to dishonor it! And I’m ashamed—ashamed—that you’re Arthur Pend Dennis!”

He broke down sobbing. The second time Arthur had reduced a man of his age to tears. That’s either a sign of great power or terrible parenting.

The moment the Major wept, Arthur stopped pacing. Like a dog who hears its master cry, he froze. Laura hovered near Helen, who sat like a porcelain doll ready to crack. Warrington, meanwhile, remained silent—until now.

“I’ve wanted to tell you all something,” he began, voice trembling slightly. “Something I’ve kept quiet because it’s sad. And because it involves another person’s secrets. But maybe it should be said. For Arthur’s sake. For all of us.”

He took a deep sip of wine—then emptied the glass. “You see me,” he said, “as a man without ambition, living in a garret, eating off plates that haven’t been washed since 1832. But I chose this. I nailed myself to this fate early on. And why? Because I once made a mistake. A terrible one.”

Everyone leaned in. Even the shadows seemed to listen.

“It was before college. I was eighteen. At a tutor’s house. I fell in love—with a woman far below my station. A yeoman’s daughter. Her parents knew who I was, and they *used* it. They flattered me, lied to me, pushed me into it. And she? She never loved me. She was forced. I thought I loved her. I was deceived. I wish I hadn’t been.”

He paused. “When I found out—when I read her letters—I saw her heart belonged to someone else. Someone of her own class. I was married to a boor. She couldn’t understand poetry. Or philosophy. Or even the concept of ‘a good sandwich.’ I grew to loathe her. And then, when my father died, I paid off my debts and gave everything I had to my wife and child—on condition they disappear and never use my name. They agreed. And they’ve kept their word. If I’d succeeded, they’d have claimed the glory. If I’d failed, they’d have taken the shame.”

He sighed. “I entered life at twenty—hopeless. Ruined. A victim of vulgar tricks. And now, I’ve learned how hard it is to forgive them.”

Pen listened, stunned. Then the Major burst out laughing. “By Gad, sir! I meant for you to marry Miss Laura here!”

“And by Gad,” said Warrington, “I owe you a thousand pounds.”

“Only a pony,” said the Major, blinking.

They both laughed. It was the first real laughter in the room since the soup course.

Helen, meanwhile, had stood up. “God bless you, Mr. Warrington,” she said, kissing his hands. Then she ran to Arthur and threw her arms around him. “I’ve wronged you, my dear. Thank God, I’ve wronged you!”

“Oh yes, mother,” Arthur said, hugging her tightly. “I am innocent. And you—you have done me wrong.”

They went into her room. The door closed. Outside, the three onlookers stood in silence, like statues carved from guilt and relief.

Inside, Arthur knelt. He told her everything—the temptation, the struggle, the moment he almost gave in. He confessed he’d threatened to take Fanny back, but only in anger. He’d never truly intend it. He’d never see her again.

But Helen said: “No. You must. And I was wrong. Proud. Culpable. I’ll write to her. I’ll give her something. And I beg your forgiveness—for reading your letter.”

Arthur sobbed. She kissed him again. And then, as the clock struck nine—just like when he was a child—she reminded him of the prayer.

So he knelt once more. He prayed. And as he said “Amen,” her head fell onto his shoulder. Her arms wrapped around him. Together, they whispered the final words: “For ever and ever.”

Then silence.

Laura heard a cry. “Laura! Laura!” She rushed in. Arthur was still kneeling. Helen was pale. Lifeless.

“Help!” Arthur cried. “She’s gone!”

Laura screamed. The Major and Warrington arrived. The servants. The priest. The local baker who’d just delivered a loaf and was now wondering if he should offer condolences.

Helen was dead. But not before experiencing joy—pure, unbroken joy. Her last breath was a blessing. Her last heartbeat, love.

They buried her beside her husband in Clavering church. Dr. Portman read the service, weeping. Only a few mourned. Most talked about the estate. Whether Arthur would sell Fairoaks. How fast he’d blow through the money. One cottager said, “Poor thing. Died of a heart complaint.” Another muttered, “I wonder if the property will cover the taxes.”

As for the rest? Forgotten by market day.

But not by Laura. Or Arthur. Or Warrington.

Among her papers, they found a note: “Letters from Laura’s father.” Arthur handed them over. They were old, faded—written before either had married. Tears had dried on the pages. Pain had healed. Love had endured.

Laura read them. And finally understood—how deeply Helen had loved her father. How she’d cherished his memory. How she’d become more than a mother to her.

And then, Arthur remembered one last thing—a small request. A legacy.

Helen had wanted to send something to Fanny Bolton.

So Arthur wrote a letter. Put it in an envelope addressed to Mr. Bows. With instructions: “Read it first.”

Dear Fanny,

I’ve received your letters—though one was delayed during my illness. (I found it in my mother’s desk. Reading it hurt.) I want to thank you—my kind nurse, my friend—for watching over me. My mother’s last words were full of gratitude for you. She said she wished she’d written to you. That she regretted treating you harshly. And that she hoped you’d accept this token of friendship.

Attached is a small sum—interest paid annually—held in trust by my friend, George Warrington. It’s not much. But it’s yours until you turn twenty-one—or change your name. Always remembered,
A. Pendennis.

Mr. Bows read it. Grumbled. “Plastering a wounded heart with a banknote? Easy sympathy.” But Fanny felt it. She knew it was goodbye.

And so, Fairoaks stood empty. No longer a home. But a memory. A monument to love, loss, and one very awkward inheritance.

And somewhere, in heaven, Helen smiled.

It so happened that on the great English holiday known as Derby Day—when all of London abandons itself to horse-racing, betting, and general merriment upon Epsom Downs—a number of our familiar characters found themselves gathered together, like a well-attended reunion of old acquaintances at a particularly rowdy school picnic.

In one comfortably open carriage, drawn by a pair of horses who looked as if they’d been plucked from a butcher’s yard and told to “get going,” sat Mrs. Bungay of Paternoster Row, resplendent in an outfit so grand it might have made Solomon himself blush and reach for a fan. Beside her, modestly draped in a shawl and looking faintly alarmed by the whole affair, was Mrs. Shandon, whom Mrs. Bungay had taken under her wing since their first meeting and now treated with the solemnity usually reserved for saints or minor royalty. Finucane, ever vigilant and slightly too eager, hovered nearby, ready to fend off any gentleman who dared approach without proper credentials.

Among those approaching was Mr. Archer, who bowed with the grace of a man who’d practiced bowing in front of a mirror every morning since 1827. He informed Mrs. Bungay of the notable figures scattered across the field: “There’s the Prime Minister!” he declared, pointing dramatically. “He just whispered to me that Borax is the horse to back. But I say Munmeer—Munmeer, madam! That’s the one.”

Mrs. Bungay blinked. “Do you know them all?”

“Why, yes,” said Archer, puffing out his chest like a rooster who’d just discovered he owns a farm. “I’ve seen them in Parliament, dined with them, danced with them—once even shared a bottle of claret with the Duke of Kent during a thunderstorm. Look yonder, in the Grand Stand—the Chinese Ambassador! With his entourage of Mandarins! I introduced him myself. The Governor-General of India sent letters, and we became bosom friends. I used to invite him to dinner, and he always brought his own cook. A very particular man, you understand.”

Mrs. Bungay leaned forward. “And did he bring good food?”

“Oh, yes,” said Archer, lowering his voice. “One day, while my wife and the Ambassador were eating gooseberries in the garden—Chinese love gooseberries, you know—the cook saw our dear little Blenheim spaniel, Fido—descended from a dog once given to the Duke of Marlborough by the King of Spain, mind you—and decided to make him into a dish. Cut his throat, skinned him, stuffed him with forcemeat, served him up as the second course. My wife didn’t speak to the Ambassador again. Never. And he hasn’t come to dine with us since. Though the Lord Mayor thought it excellent. Said it tasted rather like duck when eaten with green peas.”

Mrs. Bungay gasped. “You don’t say!”

“Fact, upon my word. And look—Lady Flamingo, seated beside him. They say she’s marrying him and returning to Pekin. She’s having her feet bound down, you see—though I doubt she’ll manage it. My wife has the smallest feet in England—wears shoes for a six-year-old child. But what’s that to a Chinese lady? She’ll cripple herself, poor thing.”

“Who’s that carriage over there?” Mrs. Bungay asked suddenly, squinting. “Mr. Pendennis is talking to someone. He was here with Mr. Warrington just now. He’s quite haughty, isn’t he? Well, he may be—he keeps very fine company. Did he inherit much money, Mr. Archer? He’s still in black, I notice.”

“Eighteen hundred a year in land,” Archer replied, “and twenty-two thousand five hundred in Three-and-a-half per Cents. Quite respectable.”

“Law!” said Mrs. Bungay. “You know everything, Mr. Archer!”

“I happen to know because I was called in about Mrs. Pendennis’s will,” Archer said proudly. “Pendennis’s uncle, the Major, never does anything without me. We tied up the property tight—no ducks and drakes allowed. Now, how do you do, my lord? Do you know that gentleman, ladies? You’ve read his speeches in the House—it’s Lord Rochester.”

“Lord Fiddlestick!” cried Finucane from the box. “Sure it’s Tom Staples of the Morning Advertiser.”

“Is it?” said Archer, blinking. “Well, I’m very short-sighted, and honestly thought it was Rochester. That gentleman with the opera-glasses—another nod—is Lord John; and the tall man beside him—don’t you know him? Sir James.”

“You know them because you see them in the House,” grumbled Finucane.

“I know them because they’re kind enough to allow me to call them my most intimate friends,” Archer corrected, as if this explained everything.

“And the Duke of Hampshire?” Mrs. Bungay asked. “What a fine-looking old gentleman! He never misses the Derby, do they say?”

“He hasn’t missed one since he was seven,” Archer said. “Rode a piebald pony with his father, the Prince of Wales, and Colonel Hanger. Only missed two races—one due to measles at Eton, and one in the Waterloo year, when he was with Wellington in Flanders.”

“Who’s that yellow carriage with the pink and yellow parasols?” Mrs. Bungay asked. “Mr. Pendennis is talking to them—so many gentlemen!”

“That’s Lady Clavering,” said Archer. “Of Clavering Park, next door to Pendennis’s estate. The young man on the box? Awfully tipsy, the little scamp. The young lady is Miss Amory—Lady Clavering’s daughter from her first marriage. She’s sweet on Pendennis, but I suspect he’s got his heart set elsewhere. You’ve heard of young Mr. Foker—the brewer? He was going to hang himself after Miss Amory refused him, but his valet cut him down just in time. Now he’s abroad under a keeper.”

“How happy that young fellow is!” sighed Mrs. Bungay. “Who’d have thought he’d turn out such a grand character? I saw his name at Court the other day—presented by the Marquis of Steyne himself. At every noble party, his name’s down like clockwork.”

“I introduced him when he first came to town,” said Archer. “Major Pendennis did the rest. Hallo! There’s Cobden! Of all people! I must go and speak to him. Good-bye, Mrs. Bungay. Good morning, Mrs. Shandon.”

Meanwhile, at another part of the course, a battered stagecoach—known as Wheeler’s Harlequin’s Head drag—was rattling along, its roof crowded with a motley crew of shabby ruffians, dubious bucks, and men whose only claim to gentility was the fact that they wore hats. As the race began, the crowd erupted into cheers, each man yelling out his favorite horse: “The Cornet!” “It’s Muffineer!” “Blue sleeves!” “Yellow cap! Yellow cap! Yellow cap!” The moment the fluttering signal revealed Podasokus as the winner, one man leapt onto the roof as if he meant to fly to London with the news.

But gravity intervened. He fell back with a thud, cracking the already frail boards beneath him. “Hurray!” he bellowed. “Podasokus wins! Supper for ten, Wheeler! Ask everyone round—damn the expense!”

The others nodded politely. “Thank you—congratulate you, Colonel,” they said, though each eye watched the others with suspicion. “The Colonel stands to win fifteen hundred,” one muttered. “And he got the odds from a good man, too.” And thus began the silent war of borrowing, where no one could be left alone lest he be cornered and asked for a loan.

Elsewhere, a far more modest vehicle—cab No. 2002—had arrived from the Strand. Inside, two ladies and a gentleman were enjoying a repast of lobster salad and bitter ale. One of the ladies, a young girl with a fresh face and rosy cheeks, sat on the box, drawing admiring glances from every dandy within sight. Her mother, meanwhile, devoured lobsters with the ferocity of a lioness, her face growing redder with each bite—until it matched the shell of the creature she was consuming.

The gentleman accompanying them—Mr. Sam Huxter—was a whirlwind of jokes and energy. He had entertained the entire journey with endless “chaff,” shouting insults at guardsmen, laughing at dustmen, firing volleys of wit at schoolgirls and urchins alike. He had filled the cab with souvenirs—pincushions, wooden apples, Jack-in-the-boxes, tiny soldiers—and even brought a gipsy woman with a tawny child to tell fortunes.

When the fortune-teller announced that the young lady should beware of a fair man who would prove false, and that she would eventually find a true one, the girl blushed deeply. Her mother and Sam exchanged glances—perhaps the conjurer had said the same thing to fifty carriages that day.

Then, through the crowd, Arthur Pendennis appeared. The girl—Fanny Bolton—turned pale. Her mother went crimson. Sam’s fists clenched in his pockets like weapons ready to strike. Pendennis, too, looked dark and suspicious—though not from jealousy, perhaps, but from moral outrage, like a Victorian detective spotting a crime before it happens.

Fanny’s heart pounded. Sam’s hands tightened. Mrs. Bolton launched into a torrent of chatter: “Oh, Mr. Pendennis! How well you look! We were just talking about you—weren’t we, Fanny? And how is Major Pendennis? And that kind Mr. Warrington? He’s so tall, he nearly broke his head on our lodge door!”

As she spoke, Fanny’s mind raced—memories of past love, sorrow, shame, and hope. There stood Pendennis—dandified, supercilious, with jet buttons, a pink flower in his coat, and gloves so tight they looked like they’d been sewn by a nervous seamstress. Sam, meanwhile, wore no gloves, boots that looked like they’d walked through a coal mine, and smelled strongly of tobacco.

After a tense silence, Fanny managed to say, “This is Mr. Samuel Huxter, sir—you knew him before, I believe. Mr. Samuel, you know Mr. Pendennis—will you take a refreshment?”

Her words, though shaky, lifted the weight from Pendennis’s shoulders. He smiled. “I’m very thirsty,” he said. “I’ll gladly drink your health, Fanny. And I hope Mr. Huxter will forgive me for being rude last time—we were both ill, and I scarcely knew what I said.”

He extended his lavender kid glove—symbol of peace. Sam, unable to resist, pulled out his fist and placed it in Pendennis’s hand. It was hot. Blackened. Left marks on the glove. He wanted to punch Pendennis in the face, right there, in front of all of England. But instead, he let it go.

Pen took a glass—any glass—and filled it with lukewarm beer, which he drank with gusto. “Delicious,” he declared.

As he sipped, a young lady in a dove-coloured dress passed by, leaning on the arm of a man with a military moustache. She gave Pendennis a mischievous glance. He laughed. The man laughed louder.

Fanny watched her go. Then she turned to Sam, her eyes bright. “What a beautiful creature! Such delicate hands!”

“It was Captain Strong,” said Mrs. Bolton. “And who was the young woman?”

“Miss Amory,” Pendennis said. “Lady Clavering’s daughter. You’ve seen Sir Francis at Shepherd’s Inn.”

Fanny immediately built a romance in her mind—love, betrayal, a grand wedding, a broken-hearted maid. Sam, meanwhile, lit a rank Cuba cigar and smoked it under her nose.

When Pendennis left, the sun seemed duller. The sky greyer. The Sticks uninteresting. The beer undrinkable. Sam forgot his pea-shooter until someone fired a volley at his face. He cursed, then burst into a sardonic laugh.

But Fanny was radiant all the way home. She coaxed, giggled, admired everything. When they arrived, Sam tried to leave with dignity. But Fanny burst into tears. “You’re a naughty, unkind thing!”

Sam, overwhelmed, held her tightly. “I’m a jealous brute,” he sobbed. “I’m unworthy of you. I shouldn’t hate Pendennis. Please—say once more that you——”

That she what? The words were lost in a kiss so close, no one could hear.

Mrs. Bolton stepped in. “Come, come, Mr. H.—no nonsense! You’ve acted like a wicked wretch!”

Later, Pendennis approached Lady Clavering’s carriage. The Major was waiting, the box occupied by young Hopeful and Captain Strong. A crowd of dandies surrounded the carriage, flirting with Miss Amory, offering bets, exchanging knowing glances.

Pendennis pushed through. Miss Amory beckoned him. “Je lay vue,” she said. “Elle a de bien beaux yeux; vous etes un monster!”

“Monster?” Pendennis laughed. “Hone suit qui mal y peens. My friend there is as safe as any young lady in Christendom. She has her mamma on one side, her pretend on the other. What harm could possibly happen?”

“One does not know,” said Miss Amory in French, “when a girl has the mind, and when she is pursued by a wicked monster like you. Imagine me finding Monsieur near a cab, with two ladies and a man—oh, such a man!—who ate lobsters and laughed, laughed!”

“It did not strike me that the man laughed,” Pendennis said. “And as for lobsters—I thought he’d have liked to eat me after them. He shook hands with me so hard he bruised my glove blue-black. He’s a young surgeon. From Clavering. Don’t you remember the gilt pestle and mortar in High Street?”

“If he attends you when you’re sick,” Miss Amory continued, “he’ll kill you. He’ll serve you right—for you are a monster.”

Pendennis frowned. “She speaks of these things too lightly,” he thought. “If I were a monster, she’d welcome me just the same. This is not how an English lady should think. Laura wouldn’t speak like this. Thank God.”

And so, as he stood there, the wind blowing his hair, the sun setting behind the hills, he felt something shift—inside, somewhere. Not quite love. Not quite regret. But something close.

CHAPTER LX. Explanations (Rewritten for Humor)

Ah, time—how it creeps along like a bad butler who’s forgotten where the wine is kept! Nearly a year has passed since we last saw our dear Arthur Pendennis, and what a transformation he’s undergone! Gone are the days of mourning—he’s traded in his black coat for a dashing blue one, as if to say, “I’ve moved on from grief, though I still haven’t quite mastered the art of not being ridiculous.” His wig? Shunned like a bad idea at a dinner party. His hair, thinner than a poorly written novel, now flutters in the breeze like a flag of surrender to aging. And then there’s the uniform—Cornet of the Clavering troop of the ——shire Yeomanry Cavalry! Not exactly the Household Cavalry, but hey, it’s something. He was presented to the Sovereign by none other than the Marquis of Steyne, a man so noble he could probably make a sandwich out of silk and call it diplomacy.

This grand gesture was insisted upon by Arthur’s uncle, Major Pendennis, whose enthusiasm for gentlemanly rituals knew no bounds. The Major believed that every young man should be properly introduced into society—preferably before he starts quoting poetry to waitresses. He also took great pleasure in announcing, with the solemnity of a weather forecast, that Arthur’s fortune had turned out “not by any means as well as he could have hoped,” amounting to a mere fifteen hundred pounds a year. Which, in the world of high society, is about as impressive as showing up to a ball wearing a hat made of cardboard and calling it “art.”

But don’t let that fool you. In the literary world, Arthur’s status has soared. Publishers now treat him like a long-lost prince, and even mothers—those formidable creatures who once wouldn’t give him the time of day—are suddenly civil. Why? Because while the dazzling Myra gets to marry an Earl (a man who probably thinks “politics” means “where the good wine is served”), poor little Beatrice Hawxby, whose shoulder is slightly higher than the other (a fact she wears like a badge of honor), needs someone reliable. And who better than Mr. Pendennis? A man with literary credentials and a face that says, “I may be awkward, but I’m trying.”

Indeed, during the first winter after inheriting his mother’s estate, Mrs. Hawxby—who clearly thought she’d found her next Jane Austen—had her daughter learn billiards from Arthur and insisted on being driven only by him in the pony carriage. She declared, with the dramatic flair of a woman who’s read too many novels, that Arthur had behaved “most infamously” in trifling with Beatrice’s feelings. But the truth? The Major, ever vigilant, had swooped in like a hawk protecting its nest, whisking Arthur away from Mrs. Hawxby’s clutches—though not from her tongue, which continued to wag like a dog’s tail at a squirrel convention.

The Major had wanted Arthur to spend Christmas at Clavering Hall, but Arthur declined. Clavering was too close to Fairoaks, the old family home now haunted by memories of lost love and tragic misunderstandings. So instead, we skip ahead to the Epsom race-ground, where the Claverings reappear—looking rather like a family that’s been through a hurricane and decided to keep living in the wreckage.

Poor Lady Clavering, once a woman whose smile could light up a room and whose grammar could trip up a professor, has had her appetite and cheerfulness severely tested. Her dinners now come with side orders of family drama, and even the finest French cook can’t make a meal palatable when your husband is gambling away the house and your daughter is flirting with the wrong kind of men.

“I’d rather have a turnip, Strong,” she sighs, staring at a pineapple like it’s plotting against her, “than all these Muscatel grapes from Clavering—if only I could eat it in peace. Oh, how much happier I was when I was a widow!” And truly, she was. Now she’s trapped in a marriage where the only thing more predictable than her husband’s debts is his ability to charm his way out of them—until he doesn’t.

The Clavering family, bless them, made a disastrous entrance into society. Their London season was met with polite indifference, followed by outright rejection. Sir Francis Clavering? Too low, too dull, too disreputable. People whispered he had “a taint”—like a bad smell you can’t quite place. Who was the Begum? Where did she come from? Why did she speak English with such strange pronunciation? And why did her daughter insist on behaving like a French aristocrat who’d just escaped from a costume party?

Sir Francis, it turns out, was not only a gambler but also a card cheat who showed the white feather—meaning he folded when the stakes got high. Hely Clinker, a fellow officer, confirmed this with the gravity of a man delivering bad news at a funeral. Lady Rockminster, who had once taken Lady Clavering under her wing, dropped her like a hot potato. The elite refused to bring their daughters to her parties. Young men attended, yes—but with the freedom of wolves at a sheep farm. Poor Lady Clavering was forced to invite “the canal”—a term for those who weren’t quite top-tier but were still better than nothing—to her parlour, because “the tip-tops” wouldn’t come.

And yet, she bore no ill will. She wasn’t proud. She didn’t think herself better than anyone. She simply followed the rules laid down by her social godmother: know whom they know, ask whom they ask. The “canal,” in fact, was far more enjoyable than “society,” which is always full of people pretending to be happy while secretly hating each other.

One young man, however, remained loyal—Harry Foker, Esq. But loyalty, as we know, often comes with consequences. His passion for Blanche Clavering became public knowledge, reached his mother’s ears, and eventually landed on the desk of Foker senior—a man whose bald head seemed to reflect the emptiness of his heart. A violent scene ensued, ending in Harry’s exile to France for a year. He was told to return and marry his cousin—or retire into private life with three hundred pounds a year and never see his father or brewery again.

So off Harry went, carrying with him the sorrow of the exiled, which, as we’re told, passes freely through customs. Even Paris looked bleak to him, and Italy’s sky seemed perpetually overcast. One might say he brought the clouds with him.

Meanwhile, Sir Francis Clavering faced his own disaster. That same year, Lord Harrowhill’s horse—named Podasokus, because apparently classical names were all the rage—won the Derby. Most experts had backed Borax, who finished nowhere near the finish line. Sir Francis, relying on “valuable information” from shady turf characters, had bet heavily against Podasokus and lost—seven thousand pounds, to be exact.

Lady Clavering, already burdened with paying off her husband’s debts, now faced another crushing blow. She had paid his money-lenders, furnished both houses, and endured countless promises of reform—only to be called upon again to bail him out. It was a cruel irony, especially since she had done everything to save him, only to find him still incapable of managing his own finances.

Enter Major Pendennis, the wise old owl of the family. He had become the Claverings’ unofficial advisor, having inspected every room of their house—including that dreaded closet where families hide their skeletons. As for Sir Francis’s finances? Well, they were so tangled in lies that even the most skilled accountant would have given up and gone to bed early.

But the Major knew Lady Clavering’s story—her past, her struggles, her first marriage, which ended in scandal. He had been in India when it happened and could tell her exactly where to find the Calcutta newspaper that reported Amory’s trial. He had kept this secret, choosing friendship over gossip, and earned her gratitude.

“Of course I have interested motives,” he confessed, “and mine, I won’t deny, is to marry my nephew to your daughter.”

Lady Clavering, surprised but willing, agreed. The Major then laid out his plan: Arthur had five hundred a year, but with Blanche’s fortune, he could rise in the world. “Your son-in-law could be a name that his sons shall be proud of,” he said, with the confidence of a man who believes in destiny and a good marriage contract.

“And what use is that seat in Parliament?” he asked, referring to Sir Francis, who rarely spoke in the House. “Arthur was famous as an orator at Oxbridge—once put his foot in the stirrup and mount him, I’ve no doubt he won’t be the last of the field.”

He urged Lady Clavering to consider a match that would benefit both families. After all, marriage isn’t about sentiment—it’s about convenience. “A girl who runs away with Jack to Gretna Green,” he said, “often runs away with Tom to Switzerland afterwards. The point is to agree to be useful to one another.”

As for Miss Amory (Blanche), she was content with Arthur as long as no one better came along. How many young ladies are like that? How many love matches survive? How many sentimental firms go bankrupt? The Major, ever practical, believed that the best marriages were built on mutual advantage—not romance.

Arthur, meanwhile, had begun to absorb his uncle’s wisdom. He spent much of the spring at Lady Clavering’s house, accepted by Blanche without being engaged, familiar without being sentimental. They met and parted with perfect good humor—no grand declarations, no tears, just quiet companionship.
