“And I,” Arthur mused, “am the same fellow who eight years ago had a Grand Passion, and last year was raging in a fever about Briseis!”

Yes, time changes us. We don’t become different people—we just reveal the parts we’ve always had. The selfishness we now feel was once the fire of ambition. The weariness we complain about is the aftermath of joy. The arrogance of success is merely the continuation of struggle.

And so, as we look back on our own lives—whether we’ve sailed into port or drowned at sea—we must remember: we are still the same person. The child who dreamed of greatness, the lover who fell out of love, the sinner who repented—all of it is part of the same journey. Whether you’re riding triumphantly into fame or clinging to a spar in the ocean, you’re still thinking of home. Still remembering childhood. Still wondering how it all turned out.

And if you’re reading this, dear reader, perhaps you’re doing the same.

Our dear, long-suffering Begum was so furious at her husband’s latest act of deceit and idiocy that she flatly refused to help Sir Francis Clavering pay his debts of honour. She declared she’d leave him to face the consequences of his unrelenting folly—and possibly even divorce him. After the disastrous day at the Derby, the poor gambler was in such a state of mental collapse he wanted to vanish from the face of the earth—avoiding both his fellow racers (who had seen him lose everything) and his wife, whose bank account he now feared might be closed forever.

The next morning, when Lady Clavering asked if Sir Francis was home, she was told he hadn’t returned—and had sent a messenger with instructions to pack his clothes and letters and send them to Short Hotel in Blackfriars. Strong knew full well that the Baronet would soon be reaching out for help, and sure enough, a note arrived begging him to visit “Mr. Francis” at the inn. The Baronet, it seemed, had a peculiar talent for lying when confronted with reality, and always chose flight over facing facts.

The hotel’s boots, carrying Clavering’s message to Grosvenor Place and returning with his carpet bag, immediately recognized the owner. He passed the news to the footman, who relayed it to the servants’ hall, then to Mrs. Bonner—the housekeeper and confidante of Lady Clavering—who finally delivered the bombshell to her ladyship. Before long, every servant in the household knew: Sir Francis was hiding under an alias at a dingy inn on the Blackfriars Road. And the coachman? He blabbed it to other coachmen, who told their masters, who whispered it at Tattersall’s, where everyone assumed Sir Francis was about to flee to the Levant—probably to avoid creditors, or maybe just to escape himself.

That same day, a suspicious number of bills piled up on the Baronet’s hall table. The French cook submitted his invoice. So did Messrs. Finer and Gimcrack, the ornamental dealers; Madame Crinoline, the famed milliner; and Miss Amory herself—whose accounts at each establishment were by no means negligible. It was clear: someone was running out of money fast.

In the afternoon, Strong dropped by Grosvenor Place as usual. He found all these documents neatly arranged in Sir Francis’s study and began leafing through them with a look of profound sorrow.

Mrs. Bonner descended upon him mid-examination. As Lady Clavering’s right hand and emotional anchor, she was firmly on her side—and more furious than the lady herself could be.

“She won’t pay,” she announced, arms crossed. “Not if I have anything to say about it. You go back to Sir Francis—this coward hiding in a cheap inn like some common sneak—and tell him we’re done. We made him a gentleman, pulled him out of prison (and others too), paid his debts again and again, gave him a seat in Parliament, houses in town and country—yet he can’t even show his face! We’ve given him his horse, his dinner, his clothes—everything! And we’re done. Our fortune is ours now. We’ll give him enough to live on, and then let him be. That’s what you tell him—from Susan Bonner.”

Lady Clavering, hearing of Strong’s arrival, summoned him upstairs. The Chevalier hoped she might be more reasonable than her loyal factotum. He’d convinced her before—many times—of Clavering’s worthiness, though not without effort. This time, he painted a bleak picture: Sir Francis was distraught, nearly suicidal, trembling at the thought of failing his creditors.

“Kill himself?” Mrs. Bonner snorted. “He’ll die happy—when he’s got one last chance to rob us.”

Strong insisted he’d found razors on the table. But Lady Clavering laughed—a cold, bitter laugh. “Oh, he’ll never harm himself. Not while there’s a shilling left to steal. His life’s safe. Depend on it. Ah, what a terrible day it was when I ever saw him!”

“The first man was better,” said the aide-de-camp. “At least he was wild—but he had courage. This one? What good does it do my lady to pay his bills, sell her diamonds, forgive him? He’ll be just as bad next year. He’ll cheat her again, rob her again, and use her money to feed thieves—though not you, Captain—you’ve been kind enough, despite our regrets.”

Strong noticed the mention of diamonds. A flicker of hope. Maybe Lady Clavering wasn’t entirely beyond mercy.

“Honestly, ma’am,” he said, genuinely moved by her patience, “anything either of you says against Clavering—or Mrs. Bonner against me—is richly deserved. We both know we’ve been lucky to survive this far. He’s treated you cruelly. But you’re the most forgiving woman in England. If you weren’t, he’d be finished. But you can’t let your son grow up with a disgraced father. Tie him down—make him promise anything. I guarantee he’ll sign.”

“And break it,” said Mrs. Bonner.

“And keep it *this* time,” Strong shot back. “He must. You should see how he wept—‘Oh, Strong,’ he said, ‘it’s not for myself—I’m thinking of my boy… and of the best woman in England, whom I’ve wronged.’ He didn’t mean to bet—he was tricked into it. Everyone in the ring was fooled. He thought it was safe. It’ll teach him for life. To see a man cry—well, it breaks your heart.”

“He doesn’t care much about making *you* cry,” Mrs. Bonner muttered. “Poor soul—look at him now, Captain.”

Later, Strong shared the scene with Clavering at Short’s Hotel, where the Baronet sat weeping into a glass of curacoa.

“If you’ve any manhood left, Clavering,” Strong said, “keep your word. Or by Heaven, I’ll turn on you and tell all.”

“What all?” Clavering gasped.

“Psha!” Strong snapped. “Do you think I’m a fool? I’ve lived long enough to know secrets. You know I only need to speak, and you’re penniless tomorrow. And I’m not the only one who knows.”

“Who else?”

“Old Pendennis. Or I’m mistaken. He recognized the man the first night he came drunk into your house.”

“He knows it?” Clavering shrieked. “Damn him—kill him!”

“You’d like to kill us all, wouldn’t you, old boy?” Strong sneered, puffing his cigar.

Clavering slammed his hand against his forehead. “Oh, Strong! If I dared, I’d end it all. I’m the most miserable dog in England. That’s why I’m reckless. That’s why I drink.” He raised the curacoa with a shaking hand. “And hang around with thieves. I know they’re thieves—every last one. But what can I do? I didn’t know—really, I didn’t! Until I saw the scoundrel, I knew nothing. And now I’ll run away—go abroad—bury myself in a forest—hang myself from a tree—oh, I’m the most wretched beggar alive!” He cried, cursed, groaned, and vowed repentance—all in the same breath.

And yet, irony struck: while Clavering sank deeper into despair, another tenant of Strong’s chambers—Colonel Altamont—was riding high. He’d won big at the Derby, thanks to a lucky bet. When Captain Clinker settled Clavering’s debts (on Lady Clavering’s orders), Altamont collected thirty-to-one odds in fifties—his winnings gleaming like gold in the sun.

His friends swarmed in to congratulate him. Tom Driver suggested reviving the sunken treasure ship in the Gulf of Mexico. Mr. Keightley pushed the Tredyddlums shares. Jack Holt offered a tobacco-smuggling scheme. Jack Rackstraw had two horses he *must* buy. Tom Fleet had a satirical paper, *The Swell*, needing £200 to become a thousand-pound-a-year venture. Little Moss Abrams urged caution: “Invest in solid bills—safe as the Bank of England, fifty per cent return!”

But Altamont resisted. He tucked his notes away and went straight to Strong’s chambers, where he “sport”-ed the outer door. Strong had warned him about his friends—and though he took twenty pounds off the Colonel’s winnings (no shame in that), he refused to let anyone take advantage of him.

Altamont wasn’t a bad sort when things were going well. He bought Grady a smart livery, made Costigan weep with gratitude over a five-pound note, gifted Mrs. Bolton a green shawl and Fanny a yellow one—both from the brightest window display in Regent Street. Then, on Miss Amory’s birthday in June, she received a parcel from “a friend”: a massive brass writing-desk with amethysts that looked like evil eyes, a musical snuff-box, two outdated Keepsakes, and two dresses in colours so outrageous they made her laugh uncontrollably.

It turned out Altamont had also bought cigars and silks from some Fleet Street charlatans. And later, Strong found him in Cheapside’s Auction Room, investing in desks, candlesticks, a dinner epergne, and a bagatelle board. The epergne stayed at chambers and became a centerpiece at Altamont’s frequent dinners—until Jack Holt remarked it looked like it had been “taken in a bill.” Which, naturally, it had.

These dinners became a weekly ritual. Sir Francis Clavering, now reduced to living in a half-shut-down house, joined them regularly. His estate was stripped bare—Mirobolant’s successor fired, footmen dismissed, one quitting because “on’y one footman was kep’”—and Lady Clavering enforced strict economy across the entire household. Major Pendennis, Strong, the lawyer, and the Begum herself carried out the reforms with military precision.

After settling Clavering’s debts—publicly scandalous, and further tarnishing his reputation—Lady Clavering fled to Tunbridge Wells in high dudgeon, refusing to see her reprobate husband. Clavering stayed in London, avoiding her wrath, slipping in and out of Parliament, playing billiards with Raff and Marker, haunting sporting pubs, or lurking near Lincoln’s Inn, waiting hours for lawyers who barely acknowledged his existence.

No wonder he loved Strong’s dinners. There, he was the guest of honour—called “Yes, Sir Francis,” “No, Sir Francis.” He cracked jokes, sang his sad little French tune after Strong’s jolly chorus and Costigan’s Irish ballads. In that warm, chaotic circle, he felt human again. At home, his grand house echoed with silence, attended only by a sour-faced housekeeper and a valet who mocked him daily.

“Damn it,” he’d complain to his friends, “that valet—must fire him. But I owe him two years’ wages. Can’t ask my lady. He brings me tea cold, with a leaden teaspoon—says she sent all the plate to the banker’s because it ain’t safe. How dare she not trust me with a single spoon? Unfair, Altamont? You know my lady’s low-born—wait, I beg your pardon—hem—still, it’s cruel. And the servants laugh—those damn rascals! I’ll break every bone in their bodies. They don’t answer my bell. And last night—my man was at Vauxhall, dancing in one of my dress-shirts and velvet waistcoats—confound him! I swear he’ll be hanged. All those infernal valets are scoundrels.”

He grew oddly fond of Altamont now. Listened eagerly to his tales: how he’d slipped aboard a ship at night to escape his wives; how they’d paddled madly after it in canoes; how he’d been lost in the bush for three months in New South Wales; how he’d met Napoleon at Saint Helena and been presented to him as a mate of the Indiaman.

Over wine, Altamont spun wild yarns—some true, many false. But Clavering drank them in, nodding solemnly, clinking glasses.

“Leave him alone,” Altamont chuckled to Strong. “I know what he’s coming to. And I know what I’m telling. I was officer on an Indiaman. I traded to New South Wales. Lost my ship. Became officer to the Nawaub. Only me and my royal master had a falling-out. Who’s hurt? Who knows? The other chap’s dead—shot in the bush, body identified at Sydney. If I thought someone’d betray me, do you think I wouldn’t wring his neck? I’ve done worse—told you about the overseer. Fair fight. He had gun and bayonet. I had an axe. Fifty men saw it—cheered me! I’d do it again. Him, wouldn’t I? I ain’t afraid. I’d kill the man who spills my secret. That’s my rule. Pass me the liquor.”

Strong stood up and removed the brandy bottle.

“Ha, ha!” Altamont laughed. “You’re right, old boy. You always keep your head. When I start pitching, I authorize you—to put away the rum-bottle.”

“Take my advice, Altamont,” Strong said gravely. “Don’t make it too tempting for Clavering to get rid of you. Who knows what he might do?”

And the event Altamont had been waiting for arrived swiftly.

One day, Strong was out on business. Clavering showed up at the chambers alone—finding only the envoy of the Nawaub.

He ranted about the world’s cruelty, his wife’s ingratitude, Strong’s betrayal. “Hundreds of pounds I’ve given Ned Strong—kept him out of jail—been his friend for life—and now he sides with her! They’ve conspired to ruin me! They don’t give me pocket-money—less than Frank has at school!”

“Why don’t you go to Richmond and borrow from him?” Altamont laughed savagely. “He wouldn’t refuse his poor old beggar of a father, would he?”

“I’ve humiliated myself,” Clavering moaned. “Look—look at these pawn-tickets! A Member of Parliament, an English Baronet—forced to pawn a drawing-room clock, a buhl inkstand, a gold duck’s-head paper-holder—worth five pounds, sold for fifteen-and-six! It’s humiliating! Made me cry—tears! And that damned valet—curse him, I wish he was hanged—he threatened to tell my lady! Said the things in my house weren’t mine to sell or throw away! By Gad, the scoundrel!”

“Cry a bit,” Altamont said kindly. “It’ll help. Why, I used to think you were a happy man. Now you’re a miserable son of a gun.”

“It’s a shame,” Clavering whined. “And I haven’t got the money to pay the cab outside. Mrs. Bolton lent me three shillings. I don’t want to ask her again. I asked Costigan—old Irish miscreant—no money. Campion’s out of town.”

“I thought you swore to your wife you wouldn’t sign any more paper,” Altamont said, puffing his cigar.

“Then why doesn’t she give me pocket-money? Damme, I need it!”

“Oh, Am——, oh, Altamont,” he sobbed. “I’m the most miserable beggar alive.”

“You want a loan?” Altamont asked. “Twenty pounds?”

“If you would,” Clavering cried, “I’d be grateful forever—forever, my dearest friend.”

“How much will you give? Fifty-pound bill, six months, half down, half in plate?”

“Yes! Yes! I’ll pay it—on the day! I’ll make it payable at my banker’s! Do anything you like!”

“Well, I was only joking. I’ll give you twenty.”

“You said a pony,” Clavering protested. “My dear fellow, you said a pony. I’ll never forget it. I’ll take it as a loan—pay you back in six months. I swear it.”

A few days after Colonel Altamont’s lucky break at Epsom, the man of fashion packed his bags and set off on his grand foreign tour—because nothing says “I’ve just won a fortune” like hopping on a boat to Antwerp. The chronicler of high society, stationed at London Bridge like some sort of social traffic cop, reported that among the distinguished passengers aboard the *Soho* were “Sir Robert, Lady, and the Misses Hodge; Mr. Serjeant Kewsy, and Mrs. and Miss Kewsy; Colonel Altamont, Major Coddy, etc.” (etc., because no one really cared about the rest). Colonel Altamont traveled with all the dignity befitting a gentleman who’d just been handed a tidy sum by fate: rich velvet coat, brandy-and-water flowing freely (and not a single bout of seasickness), and his loyal Irish servant in tow—same chap who’d once helped Captain Strong carry his laundry up the stairs of Shepherd’s Inn.

The Chevalier (who was, let’s face it, more of a character than a title) treated his departing friend to a lavish dinner at Blackwall, where they drank enough healths to drown a small village. “Strong, old boy,” said Altamont, puffing on a cigar like he owned the entire cigar industry, “if you’re short, now’s your chance. I’m your man. You’ve always been good to me, and twenty pounds? Not a problem. It’s pocket change.”

But Strong shook his head. “No need,” he said, though his voice trembled slightly. “I’m flush—well, not *quite* flush enough to pay back that last loan, but I can manage for a while.” And so they parted, shaking hands with all the warmth of two men who’d just pretended not to care about money.

Now, here’s the question: had Altamont actually become a better person now that he had cash? Or was it just that Strong, starved for kindness and respect, saw him through rose-tinted spectacles? Maybe Altamont *had* improved. Maybe wealth had polished his rough edges. But Strong couldn’t help thinking: this poor soul, this ex-convict who’d once signed his father-in-law’s name during a moment of madness (which, honestly, how many men haven’t done worse?), was ten times the man Sir Francis Clavering ever was. He had grit. He had loyalty. He’d stand by a friend and fight an enemy. Clavering? He’d run from both. And what was Clavering’s crime? Nothing worth mentioning—just being a bit reckless and signing someone else’s name. Many men had done worse and lived to tell the tale. Clavering didn’t even have the decency to hold his head up. He never did.

And so Strong, perhaps, regretted lying about not needing money. But it was a lie on the side of honor. He couldn’t bring himself to borrow again from a man who’d been cast out of polite society. Besides, he could get by. Clavering had promised him funds—though promises from Clavering were about as reliable as a weather forecast from a squirrel. Still, Strong kept hope alive. He’d catch Clavering someday, ambush one of those stray remittances, or wrangle a little something from the Baronet’s pockets. That, after all, was Strong’s life’s work: keeping Clavering afloat—and himself from drowning.

He’d grumbled about sharing rooms with Altamont, but now that the Colonel was gone, the place felt like a tomb. The loneliness gnawed at him. He missed the chatter, the shared meals, the absurdity of living with someone who wore a monocle indoors. Now he was alone, with only a servant to run errands, cook his dinners, and generally make him feel like a lord. But when the servant left? Suddenly, the Chevalier found himself painting his own boots and broiling a mutton chop over a candle flame. It was a sight—both tragic and oddly noble. A once-grand man reduced to domestic drudgery. One might almost pity him. Almost.

Of course, there was also the matter of Juanita, his Spanish wife, who’d returned to her homeland after a brief marriage that ended when she threw a dish at his head. Strong began to wonder if he should go back to Vittoria. Maybe see her again. Maybe apologize for being such a fool. But then again, maybe not. Women were complicated. Especially ones who wielded crockery like weapons.

As for Clavering—no funds from him. The twenty-five pounds Altamont had sent had vanished into the ether faster than a politician’s promise. Clavering had spent it all on a river trip with a group of sporting gentlemen who evaded the police and landed in Essex, where they arranged a boxing match between Billy Bluck and Dick the cabman. Clavering backed Dick, who dominated for thirteen rounds—until, tragically, Billy delivered a fatal blow to the windpipe. “It’s always my luck,” Clavering moaned. “I thought I was guaranteed thirty pounds. And now I owe Lightfoot fourteen—damn him! He duns me like a bloody parrot. If only I could get a bill drawn up—or squeeze something out of my lady!”

“Half of it, Ned,” he begged. “I swear on my honour, I’ll give you half if you can find someone to do us a little fifty.”

But Ned—Mr. Strong—was firm. “I gave my word as a gentleman,” he said, “that I would not take part in any more of your bill schemes. And I meant it.”

“And I mean to keep mine,” said Strong. “I’ll black my own boots till the day I die before I break that promise.” And worse—he vowed he’d warn Lady Clavering if he ever heard her husband planning to betray her. That was the kind of integrity that made people pause.

Clavering went into full meltdown mode. Tears, curses, threats of suicide. He implored Strong, his “dearest Ned,” not to abandon him. Then, as he descended the stairs of Shepherd’s Inn, he turned around and screamed, “You infernal villain! Traitor! Coward! May you rot in hell!”—before adding, with dark satisfaction, “But I hope you live long enough for me to get my revenge.”

Meanwhile, in Strong’s chambers, Clavering met a new regular: Mr. Samuel Huxter of Clavering. Young Sam, who’d once poached walnuts in the park and watched Sir Francis drive past with four horses and powdered footmen, now adored his “Member.” He introduced himself with trembling pride: “Son of Mr. Huxter, market-place. Father attended Sir Francis’s keeper when his gun exploded—lost three fingers. Proud to meet Sir Francis!” Clavering, bless him, accepted the homage with a nod and a smile.

Huxter later told the boys at Bartholomew’s how great Sir Francis was—“a thoroughbred un, a proper English gentleman!”—to which Fanny, the lodge keeper’s daughter, replied, “He’s ojous. I don’t know why, but I can’t abear him. He’s wicked, low, mean. Mar refused him twice. Took three months to get seven shillings!”

“Don’t say ‘er’ but ‘her,’ ‘borrer’ but ‘borrow,’” Huxter corrected gently.

“Well then, her, borrow, hactually—there! You stoopid, stoopid, old foolish stoopid!” Fanny laughed, pulling his hair and patting his chin. “He borrows from everyone. Mar wouldn’t lend him a penny. Not one!”

And so the grammar lesson ended in laughter and affection—a rare thing in a world where money ruled and manners were often just masks.

Mrs. Bolton, of course, was present. And time had softened Fanny’s heart. She’d grown fond of Dr. Sam. Time does that. It dulls regrets, soothes wounds, turns bitterness into tenderness. Just as it turns old men into saints in their memories.

Ah, the elderly Dives, sitting surrounded by flattery, servants bowing, tenants cap-in-hand—how often he thinks: *This respect is temporary. When I’m gone, it will pass to my son.* The wine in the cellar? He’ll drink it. The estate? He’ll inherit it. The reverence? It will transfer like a deed. The heir becomes the new sun. Men flock to him. The glory shifts. The successor whispers: “Make ready to go. I am waiting.”

What has this to do with our story? Does it excuse Pen for wearing a white hat and mourning his mother lightly? No. Pen’s love for his mother was deep, enduring. She was his saint. Her memory lived in his heart, watching over him—judging, comforting, forgiving. If he sinned, her eyes looked on sadly. If he triumphed, she smiled. If he fell, she pleaded. If he suffered, she cheered. She was with him always—until death.

Is this sentimental nonsense? Perhaps. But even the most worldly man needs moments of reflection. Ask yourself: do we not live in the dead? Do we not prove God through love?

Pen and Warrington spoke of such things often—quietly, solemnly. Pendennis’s mother was canonized in his memory. Lucky the man who knows such women. Heaven sends them as gifts—beacons of innocence, love, beauty.

But suppose, just suppose, Major Pendennis walked in during one of these philosophical sessions. Arthur and Warrington would have snapped shut like books. They’d switch to opera gossip, parliamentary debates, or Miss Jones’s marriage to Captain Smith—anything to avoid the awkward silence of deep feeling.

So let us return to the real world—where money matters, reputations are fragile, and friendship is hard to keep.

When Arthur Pendennis inherited his estate, he handled it with modesty. Warrington praised him. His uncle, Major Pendennis, frowned. “Why not ride in a cabriolet? Why not show your status?” Arthur laughed. “I’m too clumsy. The omnibus would cut me in two. And I’d rather not look like an apothecary driven by my servant.”

“No,” said the Major firmly. “You mustn’t demean yourself. You’re a gentleman—never forget it.”

Arthur countered with a quote he’d heard his uncle use often: “In the society I move in, who cares about my humble beginnings? It’s absurd to compete with the great. All they expect is decent manners.”

“But you should join better clubs,” the Major insisted. “Give dinners. Move out of that garret.”

And so Arthur compromised: second floor in Lamb Court. Warrington stayed put. The two friends refused to part. Cultivate friendships young, reader. They’re formed in generous youth. Later ones are weaker—like hands that’ve shaken too many careless palms.

Pen made acquaintances daily. He was jovial, easygoing. But no one like Warrington. They lived almost as one—riding on the same horse (Pen’s was always at Warrington’s disposal), sharing chambers, servants, and secrets.

Warrington had met Pen’s Grosvenor Place crowd during their disastrous season in London. He didn’t like them. “The world is right,” he said. “Lady Clavering and her daughter? Unfit company. That girl talks to men she shouldn’t. Did you see those two reprobates leering over her carriage?”

“The Begum is innocent,” Pen protested. “She doesn’t know anything about Captain Blackball’s trial or Charley Lovelace’s scandal. You think honest ladies read the *Chronique Scandaleuse*?”

“Would you want Laura Bell near those men?” Warrington shot back, face reddening. “Would you let a woman you loved be tainted by them? She may be ignorant—but ignorance isn’t virtue. She’s not a lady.”

“She’s as moral as Lady Portsea, who hosts balls for the whole world,” Pen retorted. “As refined as Mrs. Bull, who breaks English but feeds dukes. She pays her husband’s debts. Spoils her son. Does her best. Why judge her for not reading Wordsworth?”

“Because a gentleman chooses his companions wisely,” Warrington growled. “You call me a cynic? But look at you—you’re defending vulgarity as if it were virtue. Is your ambition just good claret and a silver plate? Are you proud to dine with anyone who serves a stalled ox?”

“Because I like raw steak and a pipe,” Pen snapped, “don’t assume I despise refinement. Who goes around praising the people they meet every day? I accept A.’s hospitality. I don’t pretend to repay it with friendship. We part without grief. We meet, we’re glad. That’s enough.”

“You’re your uncle’s pupil,” Warrington sighed. “You speak like a worldling.”

“And why not?” asked Pendennis. “Why pretend the world isn’t what it is? I’ve seen more of it than you have in your garret. I accept it. If the world’s broken, do I have the strength to fix it?”

“I don’t think you do,” muttered Warrington.

“If I doubt I’m better than others,” Arthur continued, “I also doubt they’re better than me. Reformers start loud, end quiet. Radicals become placemen. Liberals turn Conservatives. Look at Thiers, Guizot—their conduct isn’t treason. It’s submission. They march with the world, not ahead of it. They compromise. They wait. They adapt.”

“The Right Honourable Arthur Pendennis could not speak better,” Warrington said, “if he were Chancellor of the Exchequer.”

And so the debate ended—not with agreement, but with mutual recognition: the world was messy. And they both, in their own ways, were trying to survive it.

The more Major Pendennis learned about the Clavering family—through Strong, and through his own discreet inquiries as a friend of the household—the more he began to wonder if he’d ever really wanted to help Arthur at all. The idea of marrying Arthur to Lady Clavering’s daughter was beginning to look less like a romantic solution and more like a financial disaster wrapped in lace and embroidery. After all, what kind of father-in-law would you want? One who’d been convicted of murder (though conveniently vanished), and another whose name had a footnote like “sus. per coll.” after it, as though he’d been erased from history by a particularly spiteful clerk?

Now, Amory might be a charming rogue, but if he ever stepped foot back under British law, he’d probably end up on the gallows with a crowd cheering. And Altamont? Well, Altamont was a man who seemed to live in a perpetual state of near-discovery, always one step ahead of justice—and just far enough behind decency to make anyone with half a mind think twice before introducing him into polite society.

And yet, even if the Begum were to pay off Clavering’s debts ten times over, she’d still be left with nothing but an empty purse and a husband who couldn’t stop signing his name to bills like a man possessed. Her heirs would inherit a title and a scandal, not a fortune. And Miss Amory? She’d bring her husband not a seat in Parliament, but a pedigree so tarnished it looked like someone had tried to scrub out the last male ancestor with sandpaper.

But then again, there was another option—something the old schemer hadn’t quite ruled out yet. Something that might come to light if one happened to overhear a conversation between Major Pendennis and the honourable Baronet himself, Member for Clavering.

When a man gets into financial trouble, he doesn’t just disappear—he *vanishes*. Like a puff of smoke from a cigar, or a bad decision from a man’s memory. I once knew a gentleman of fashion, known for his fine horses and his impeccable waistcoats, who vanished from the clubs and parks and turned up one day, perfectly cheerful, at an eighteen-penny ordinary in Billingsgate. Another fellow—very clever, very witty, and possibly a literary man (though some critics would say that alone is proof of guilt)—sent me his address while on the run: “The Fox under the Hill,” tucked away in a dark archway in the Strand. He was hiding, yes—but also dining, drinking, and laughing, as though nothing had happened.

Such men have homes, but they’re never home. They have addresses, but only fools go there hoping to find them. Only a few loyal souls know where to look. So when Sir Francis Clavering started arguing with his wife and losing money faster than a gambler on a bad night, finding him became impossible. “Ever since I got him for my book—fourteen pounds—he don’t come home till three o’clock,” Mr. Lightfoot told Morgan. “Pretends to be asleep when I bring his water in the morning, and dodges out when I’m downstairs.” Then he announced he’d be moving to Lady Clavering’s house as butler and marrying the old woman—clearly a man who believed in loyalty, even if he didn’t practice it.

In much the same way, after his quarrel with Strong, the Baronet avoided him like a man avoiding a tax collector—or conscience. He fled to hidden corners, changed his habits, and hoped no one would notice. But the truth is, when you’ve done something shameful, you don’t just move—you hide. And sometimes, you hide so well that even your own valet thinks you’re dead.

So when Major Pendennis decided he must speak with this elusive representative of the House of Commons, it took more than one attempt. He invited him to dinner at Greenwich—“a few friends,” he said, as though the entire gathering would consist of him, a plate of whitebait, and a silent waiter. The Baronet accepted, then failed to appear. The Major ate alone, which was hardly a tragedy, but it did leave him feeling slightly absurd.

Another time, the Major wrote a note: “Ten minutes’ talk?” The Baronet replied instantly: “Four o’clock tomorrow, Bays’s. Precisely.” He underlined “precisely” like a man afraid of being late. But when four o’clock came, so did the inevitable silence. No Clavering. Not even a ghost.

It was clear: this was not a man to be found. It was a man to be hunted.

Just before sunset, on the day when Strong had given the Chevalier a blessing in front of him and a curse behind, Sir Francis Clavering—having sworn on his knees to his wife’s lawyers that he would accept no more bills—signed a piece of stamped paper anyway. Moss Abrams, his trusted friend and accomplice, carried it off, promising it would be “done” by someone with connections. And Strong heard about it—right in the back parlour of Mr. Santiago’s cigar shop, where the Chevalier spent most evenings pretending to read poetry while actually listening to gossip.

“He’s at it again,” Mr. Santiago told Strong. “Clavering and Abrams were in my parlour. Moss sent my boy for a stamp. Must’ve been fifty pounds. The Baronet told him to date it two months back. He’ll pretend it’s an old bill, the one he forgot during the settlement. I bet they’ll give him more money now.”

Ah, the joys of being a man whose signature is passed around like a novelty at a party. When you’re constantly writing “I promise to pay” on pieces of paper, you become famous—not in the good way, but in the way people whisper about you in dark corners.

Mr. Santiago’s shop was just off St. James’s Street, near where Major Pendennis lived. That evening, as the Major walked toward his lodgings with the dignity of a man who has never lost a fight, Strong strode past him, glowing with rage and smelling faintly of Havana.

“Confound these young men,” thought the Major. “They poison everything with their smoke. Here comes one with mustachios and a cigar. Every man with a cigar and mustachios is a low fellow. Oh! It’s Mr. Strong. I hope you are well?”

He bowed with the grace of a man who believes he’s doing a favour by speaking to someone who isn’t a duke.

Strong stopped. “Bad news, sir,” he said. “About our friends at Tunbridge Wells. Clavering’s at it again.”

“Indeed!” said the Major, suddenly interested. “Come in. Come into my lodging.”

They entered. The Major sat. Strong unburdened himself. “No promises bind him. Remember when we met with Lady Clavering’s lawyer? He wouldn’t settle for ‘my word’—he had to swear on a Bible, ring the bell for it, and threaten perdition. Now he’s signed another bill. And will sign more. He’ll deceive anyone—his wife, his child, his best friend. There’s a bill due next week—mine and his.”

“I thought we paid all.”

“No,” Strong said, blushing. “He asked me not to mention it. I had half the money ready. But they’ll come down on me. I don’t care—I’m used to it. It’s Lady Clavering I feel for. That good-natured woman, who’s paid him out of prison a dozen times, ruined by his heartlessness. A bunch of rogues get his money. And he throws honest men over. Would you believe it? He took money from Altamont.”

“Altamont?” asked the Major, feigning ignorance. “That singular man who came tipsy once to Sir Francis’s house?”

“I don’t know who he is,” said Strong, suspiciously.

“Ah,” said the Major, “I suppose—I mean, I only suppose—that in our friend Clavering’s life—well, let’s say he’s a rather loose fish. And such men often have secrets. Perhaps this Altamont knows something about Clavering. Has leverage. Gets paid for silence. I know men of the best families who pay through the nose that way. But it’s none of my business. I dine with a man, I don’t pry into his past. I’m interested in Lady Clavering’s sake. In her daughter’s. She’s a charming creature. When she asked me to look into things, I did. And I’ll do it again, if I can be useful. But—by the way—this Mr. Altamont. Do you know him? Is he in town?”

“I don’t know that I’m called upon to know where he is,” said Strong, rising sharply and grabbing his hat.

The Major’s tone shifted instantly from condescension to warm camaraderie. “Ah, Captain Strong, you’re cautious. Quite right. We don’t know what ears walls may have. As a man of the world, and an old soldier—a distinguished soldier, I’ve been told—you know better than to waste your fire. You may have your ideas; I may put two and two together. But there are things a man shouldn’t know. Things I won’t know until I have reason. And I believe that’s your maxim too.”

“Certainly,” said Strong, dryly.

“Delighted to hear it. Delighted that an old brother soldier agrees with me. And how lucky this meeting is! Good evening. Thank you. Morgan, show the door to Captain Strong.”

Strong left, baffled. And Morgan, standing silently by the door, equally baffled. For Morgan had heard nearly all of it—and understood almost none.

Later, Morgan asked Lightfoot: “Who is this Altamont? Know anything about him and Strong?”

“Strong’s his man of business,” said Lightfoot. “Draws the Governor’s bills, indosses ’em, does odd jobs. Altamont’s in it too. Kite-flying takes three men to start. Altamont bet on the Derby and won. I wish the Governor could win something. I’d like my book paid.”

“Do you think Lady Clavering will pay again?” Morgan asked.

“Find out,” said Morgan. “And I’ll make it worth your while.”

Morgan was rich. Not because he was a nobleman, but because he was a valet with a head for numbers. Over the past year, he’d quietly accumulated a tidy sum—fifty or a hundred pounds—by buying up Sir Francis Clavering’s bills, knowing full well that Lady Clavering would pay them. He’d learned the trade from other valets, who whispered secrets like “This man’s wife pays his debts” or “That one’s bankrupt but still lives like a king.”

When Arthur Pendennis came into his inheritance, Morgan approached him with a request: “I’ve a little sum—perhaps fifty or a hundred pounds. Could you help me invest it? The gentlemen in the Temple know about affairs and business. I’d be most grateful.”

Arthur laughed. “I know nothing about money.”

Morgan, unfazed, said, “Then I shall be most grateful anyway. And if you ever need a little cash before your rents come in, perhaps you’ll remember your uncle’s faithful servant has some money he’d like to lend.”

Arthur nearly snapped back—*Borrow from my uncle’s valet?*—but caught himself. The joke was too good. And maybe Morgan wasn’t joking at all. So he declined politely and told his uncle.

The Major chuckled. “I always said Morgan was richer than I am. He bought the house in Bury Street—where I’m a lodger—and made a fortune trading in Clavering’s paper. He’s a dev’lish sly fellow. And a dev’lish rich one. Many a nobleman would love such a valet.”

On the day of Strong’s visit, the Major remembered Morgan’s wealth and decided to tease him.

“I hear you’ve got money to invest, Morgan,” he said.

“It’s Mr. Arthur who’s been telling,” Morgan thought.

“I’m glad my place is such a good one.”

“Thank you, sir,” Morgan replied. “I’ve no complaints.”

“You’re a good fellow. And I’m glad you’re doing well. Just don’t go starting a public-house.”

A public-house? Morgan thought. Me? The old fool. If I were ten years younger, I’d set up in Parliament before I died. “No, sir. I’ve got my savings safely invested.”

“You do a little discounting, eh?”

“Yes, sir. A very little.”

“Might I ask—about Sir Francis Clavering’s paper? Do you think he’s any good? Will Lady Clavering pay anymore?”

“What? You’ve already done something?”

“A little, sir. Having some information, and acquaintance with the family through your kindness, I laid my money on. Bought his bills—many with his name and Mr. Strong’s. And I know about the blow-up in Grosvenor Place. So if you could tell me whether Lady Clavering will pay again…”

The Major stared. He’d never imagined his valet was a financier. But then again, he admired men who made money. Even if they did it under his nose.

“Well, Morgan,” he said, “I can’t ask how rich you are. The richer, the better. And if I can help you, I will. But frankly—if Lady Clavering asks me whether to pay more, I’d advise against it. Though I fear she will. And you know Sir Francis is back to his reckless ways?”

“At his old games, sir. Can’t stop him.”

“Mr. Strong mentioned Moss Abrams held a bill. Do you know him? The amount?”

“Don’t know the bill. Know Abrams well.”

“I wish you’d find out. And tell me where I can see Sir Francis.”

“Thank you, sir. Yes, sir. I will.”

Morgan left. The Major sat, stunned.

Next morning, Morgan returned: “I saw Abrams. The bill’s for fifty pounds. And Sir Francis will be at the Wheel of Fortune Tavern at one o’clock.”

At one o’clock, the Baronet sat in the tavern’s back room, surrounded by spittoons, boxing prints, and the lingering smell of tobacco. He was reading Bell’s Life in London, stained with beer.

And then in walked the Major.

“So it’s you, old boy?” said Clavering, thinking it was Abrams.

“How do you do, Sir Francis? I wanted to see you. Followed you here.”

Clavering’s face fell.

Now that he had his man, the Major went straight to work.

“I know,” he said, “who you’re here for. And why.”

“It ain’t your business,” Clavering muttered. “Why are you following me? Meddling? I’ve never done you harm. Never borrowed your money. I don’t choose to be domineered over. If Lady Clavering wants to talk, let it be through lawyers. I’d rather not have you.”

The Major smiled. “You’re not going to escape me, my dear Sir Francis. Not today.”

On a picturesque common near Tunbridge Wells—where the air is so pure it makes one sneeze, and the scenery so charming it nearly ruins your digestion—Lady Clavering had secured a villa that looked like a fairy-tale cottage if you squinted just right. After her latest marital skirmish (a mere scratch compared to the war of attrition waged during the London season), she fled there with all the grace of a disgraced queen escaping a mob. Miss Amory, naturally, tagged along like a well-bred shadow. Master Clavering, the family’s only male heir, arrived for the holidays, bringing with him the usual chaos of schoolboy energy—though Blanche’s primary occupation was not to play with him, but to quarrel with him as though they were rivals in a bad farce. But this was merely domestic theatre, and the young gentleman, being a boy of thirteen and therefore constitutionally opposed to home life, preferred cricket, horses, and the general mayhem of Tunbridge Wells society.

The Begum’s house, a sort of gilded pigsty of hospitality, was perpetually overrun by a pack of thirteen-year-old gentlemen who ate tarts like they were sacred relics, drank champagne like it was water, raced on the lawn like their lives depended on it, smoked cigars indoors like they were in a Turkish bath, and generally made such a mess that poor Mrs. Smirke could barely breathe without choking on secondhand pipe smoke and juvenile idiocy. Blanche, however, found this company utterly unbearable. She did not like young gentlemen of thirteen—unless they were particularly good at singing or had nice hair.

For Blanche, change was the spice of life—any change, even poverty, a cottage, bread and cheese, or a dungeon with bread and water. A night in a dungeon might have been delightful, provided there was a moonbeam and a romantic prisoner. So the move to Tunbridge Wells was not unwelcome; indeed, it felt like liberation from a world of dullness and duty.

She wandered through the woods, sketching trees and farmhouses with the solemnity of an artist preparing for a posthumous exhibition. She read French novels—mostly ones about unrequited love and tragic misunderstandings—and drove into Tunbridge Wells whenever something interesting appeared: a play, a ball, a conjurer, or a musician. She slept a great deal—more than any woman should, especially one who claimed to be “so full of spirit.” She quarreled with her mother and brother in the mornings, then attended the village school, where she first charmed the girls and tormented the mistress, then scolded the girls and laughed at the teacher. She was constant at church, of course—because everyone must be seen somewhere, and church was less likely to involve drunkenness than the local tavern.

The church itself was a tiny Anglo-Norman bijou, built “the day before yesterday” according to local legend, decorated with painted windows, carved saints’ heads, gilt scripture texts, and open pews that looked like they’d been designed by someone who’d never heard of privacy. Blanche immediately took up needlework and began crafting a high-church altar cover with the precision of a woman planning a royal coronation. She became a saint in the eyes of Mr. Smirke, the clergyman, whom she coaxed, wheedled, and fondled with such artful charm that poor Mrs. Smirke—wife of our old friend Smirke, Pen’s former tutor and Helen’s would-be suitor—was reduced to near madness with jealousy.

Smirke, once a man of modest piety and even more modest neckties, had undergone a spiritual metamorphosis after his wife’s death. He cut off his coat collar, let his hair grow long enough to qualify as a wig, abandoned his forehead curl (a relic of youth), and went tieless—like a man who’d lost faith in fashion and civilization alike. He fasted on Fridays, read the Roman Hours, and announced he was ready to hear confessions in the vestry. The most harmless creature alive, he was denounced as a Jesuit spy by Muffin of the Dissenting chapel and Mr. Simeon Knight of the old church. “Lord! Lord!” people whispered. “What would his mother have said if she’d seen a table called an altar? Candlesticks? Letters signed ‘On the Feast of Saint So-and-so’?” All these things Smirke did—his faithful wife following him like a loyal shadow.

When Blanche held a two-hour conference with him in the vestry, Belinda paced outside on the grass, where only two little gravestones stood. She wished there were a third—one for herself. “Only,” she thought, “he’d probably offer it to *her*—that creature who has bewitched him in less time than it takes to say ‘Grace.’ No, I’ll retire. I’ll go into a convent. I’ll profess and leave him.”

Such were the thoughts of Smirke’s wife and neighbors—some believing him in direct correspondence with the Pope, others mourning the errors of his ways. Yet Smirke meant no harm. The post-office never delivered letters from Rome. He truly believed Blanche to be pious, gifted, and angelic—at least until she lost her temper, rapped a child’s knuckles, and ruined the schoolmistress’s dignity. Then he began to doubt. And then he grew tired.

Blanche had a way of inspiring admiration and then boredom in men. She’d fling out all her graces at once—jewels, smiles, coquettish glances, sweet words—then drop them like hot potatoes when she got bored. And men, equally easily bored, dropped her too. It was a happy night for Belinda when Blanche left. Smirke, blushing and sighing, confessed he’d been deceived. “I thought her endowed with precious gifts,” he said. “Now I fear they’re mere tinsel. I thought she was serious about religion—now I suspect she treats it like a game. She lost her temper with the schoolmistress, and beat Polly Rucker’s knuckles cruelly.”

Belinda flew into his arms. No more talk of graves or veils. He tenderly kissed her forehead. “There is none like thee, my Belinda,” he declared, gazing upward with dramatic intensity, “precious among women!”

As for Blanche, she forgot them both the moment she turned the corner. But when Arthur Pendennis came down to spend a few days at Tunbridge Wells, the stage of indifference had not yet set in.

Smirke still believed Blanche to be an angel. He sat listening to her sing in summer evenings, mouth agape, tea forgotten, butter uneaten. He’d heard opera once—on the day he’d accompanied Helen and her son to a play in Chatteris—and while he admitted it was impressive, he now swore nothing compared to Blanche’s music. She was gifted. She had a soul. She had talents. She had, outwardly, the most heavenly disposition. He spoke of her thus to Arthur, who arrived with a cordial greeting and a heart full of affection for anyone who loved his mother.

Arthur, always amused by eccentricity, found Smirke endlessly entertaining—his airs, his simplicity, his long hair, his blank stock, his genuine kindness. And Smirke’s praise of Blanche surprised and pleased him. He began to regard her with new respect.

Truthfully, Blanche was delighted to see Arthur. Like any sensible woman in the country, she welcomed a man from the city who brought news, stories, and the sound of London slang—“the darling jargon,” as she called it, “so dear and indispensable to us Londoners, so incomprehensible to those outside the world.” The first evening Arthur arrived, he kept her laughing for hours after dinner. She sang with renewed vigor. She didn’t scold her mother; instead, she kissed her, much to the Begum’s astonishment. At bedtime, she said, “Deja!” with the prettiest expression of regret, squeezed Arthur’s hand warmly, and he returned the pressure with equal warmth.

Arthur, being of a nature easily dazzled by moderately bright eyes, thought, “She is very much improved. Very much. I suppose the Begum won’t mind my smoking with the window open. She’s a jolly good old woman, and Blanche is immensely improved. I liked her manner with her mother tonight. I liked her laughing way with that stupid young cub of a boy—though they oughtn’t to let him get drunk. She sang those little verses beautifully—they were devilish pretty verses, though I say it who shouldn’t say it.” And he hummed a tune she’d set to some of his own lines.

“Ah! What a fine night!” he mused. “How jolly a cigar is at night! How pretty that little Saxon church looks in the moonlight! I wonder what old Warrington’s doing? Yes, she’s a dayvlish nice little thing, as my uncle says.”

“Oh, heavenly!” came a voice from a clematis-covered casement nearby—the voice of the author of *Mes Larmes*. Arthur burst into laughter.

“Don’t tell about my smoking,” he said, leaning out of his window.

“Oh! Go on! I adore it,” the lady of *Mes Larmes* replied. “Heavenly night! Heavenly, heavenly moon! But I must shut my window—not to talk to you on account of *les moeurs*. How droll they are, *les moeurs*! Adieu.”

And Arthur began to sing the Goodnight to Don Basilio.

The next day, they walked together through the fields, laughing and chattering—the gayest pair of friends imaginable. They talked about their youth, and Blanche grew prettily sentimental. They discussed Laura, dearest Laura—Blanche had loved her like a sister. Was she happy with Lady Rockminster? Wouldn’t she come and stay? Oh, what walks they’d take! What songs they’d sing—the old, old songs! Laura’s voice was splendid. Did Arthur—she must call him Arthur—remember the songs they sang in the happy old days, now that he was such a great man and had such a *succès*?

The day after, they took a ramble through the woods to Penshurst, admired the park and hall, and then came the conversation with the curate—which made Arthur think more and more.

“Is she all this perfection?” he asked himself. “Has she become serious and religious? Does she tend schools and visit the poor? Is she kind to her mother and brother? Yes, I’m sure of that—I’ve seen her.”

Walking with his old tutor over the little parish, visiting the school, Arthur found Blanche seated among the children, instructing them with patient dignity. He imagined her as simple, kind, unspoiled—truly innocent of the world’s corruption.

“And do you really like the country?” he asked her.

“I should like never to see that odious city again. O Arthur—that is, Mr.—well, Arthur, then—one’s good thoughts grow up in these sweet woods and calm solitudes, like those flowers which won’t bloom in London, you know. The gardener comes and changes our balconies once a week. I don’t think I shall bear to look London in the face again—its odious, smoky, brazen face! But, heigho!”

“Why that sigh, Blanche?”

“Never mind why.”

“Yes, I do mind why. Tell me, tell me everything.”

“I wish you hadn’t come down,” she said—and a second edition of *Mes Soupirs* burst forth.

“You don’t want me, Blanche?”

“I don’t want you to go away. I don’t think this house will be very happy without you, and that’s why I wish you’d never come.”

*Mes Soupirs* were laid aside. *Mes Larmes* began.

Ah! What answer is given to those in the eyes of a young woman? What method is used to dry them? What happened?

O ringdoves and roses, O dews and wildflowers, O waving greenwoods and balmy airs of summer! Here were two battered London rakes, taking themselves in for a moment, and fancying they were in love with each other—like Phillis and Corydon!

When one thinks of country houses and country walks, one wonders that any man is left unmarried.

Arthur Pendennis, ever the model of candour and open-heartedness—especially when it came to his pal Warrington—was oddly tight-lipped about one particular episode from his recent trip to Tunbridge Wells. He chatted freely about bumping into his old tutor Smirke, his wife’s latest antics, the architectural splendour of his Anglo-Norman church, and his dramatic departure from Clapham to Rome. But when Warrington asked about Blanche? Suddenly Arthur became a master of evasion.

“Oh, she’s a nice little thing,” he’d say, scratching his head like a man who’d just forgotten the name of a distant cousin. “Clever, too. With proper guidance, she might even make a decent wife someday. But no—no marriage plans for me. My romantic days are over. I’m perfectly content with my current state of affairs.” And so forth.

Meanwhile, in the postbox at Lamb Court, Temple, arrived a steady stream of tiny satin envelopes, sealed with an elegant cipher that looked suspiciously like a fancy doodle by someone who’d spent too long practicing calligraphy after a bottle of sherry. If Warrington had been half as curious as he was judgmental, he might’ve noticed these letters were addressed to someone whose initials were B.A. — which, if you’re into cryptic romance, could stand for “Blanche Amory” or “Best Advice.” Either way, Mr. Pen was clearly corresponding with a lady of mystery.

And how did he reply? Oh, with the full panoply of gallant letter-writing: witty banter, gossip from town, clever quips, and—most dangerously—little verses. Poems! Yes, the very same love poems that later graced the pages of *Roseleaves*, that delightful annual edited by Lady Violet Lebas and illustrated with portraits of noblewomen by the legendary Pinkney. These weren’t written in some poetic fever dream—they were first sent to Blanche via post, likely wrapped around a cracker and delivered by a nervous boy with a bad case of hiccups.

“Poetry’s all well and good,” said old Major Pendennis, catching his nephew scribbling one of these tender effusions at the Club while waiting for dinner. “But mind, Pen, don’t go writing anything that’ll come back to haunt you. Letters can be dangerous things. I never wrote a single line that could incriminate me. And believe me, I’ve had experience with women.”

The Major, now slightly more garrulous than dignified, proceeded to share a series of cautionary tales—some of which were probably true, others possibly embellished by years of wine and regret. Like the time young Spoony wrote overly passionate verses to widow Naylor, only to have her brother, Colonel Flint, show up unannounced and demand he marry her immediately—resulting in a union with a woman old enough to be his grandmother. Or the time Louisa Salter, having won Sir John Bird’s heart, had her letters discovered by Hopwood of the Blues, leading to a sudden withdrawal and eventual marriage to Miss Stickney of Lyme Regis—a woman so plain she once caused a pigeon to fly away mid-coo.

Pen laughed politely, blushing slightly—perhaps not because of his uncle’s advice, but because he already knew what he was doing wrong. In fact, he was being *too* careful. His letters to Blanche were carefully worded, polite, and utterly safe. No confessions. No declarations. Just charming nonsense that could be read as flirtation or friendship, depending on your mood.

“I remember the lesson I learned in Lady Mirabel’s affair,” Arthur said, feigning humility. “I’m not going to be caught again, Uncle.”

Old Pendennis puffed out his chest like a satisfied hen. “Good lad! Progress! You’re becoming a man of the world!”

If Warrington had been consulted, he would’ve scoffed. “You know what?” he’d say, “the boy’s clumsy, honest letters are better than this polished deceit. Real love doesn’t hide behind euphemisms. A coward—or a knave—uses subterfuge. But Pen? He’s guilty. So he keeps quiet.”

Meanwhile, Captain Strong—once the sturdy pillar of Shepherd’s Inn—was facing a crisis of his own. After Altamont left for his foreign tour, Sir Francis Clavering retired to the country, leaving Strong alone to face the music. When Altamont offered him a loan before departing, Strong refused—on principle. A noble gesture, yes, but one that soon came back to bite him in the posterior.

He regretted it instantly. Why refuse kindness? Why reject water from a friendly hand just because it was slightly muddy? Strong’s conscience gnawed at him. Had he taken Altamont’s money, it might’ve ended up in a gambling den anyway. Instead, it stayed in Altamont’s pocket—and Strong’s financial situation began to resemble a sinking ship.

Rumours spread among tradesmen: “Strong and Clavering have split. Strong’s paper is worthless.” The once-trusted Captain found himself besieged. Tailors, bootmakers, pastry cooks—all descended upon Shepherd’s Inn, bills in hand, faces like thunder. One particularly persistent pastry cook even brought his entire staff to hold a daily levee on the stairs.

Strong, broke and cornered, retreated into his chambers—the Englishman’s castle, as they say. He barred both doors, peeked through the letter-box curtain like a fox in a burrow, and watched as duns banged their fists and cursed in vain. It was comedy gold: furious clerks, grim-faced collectors, all thwarted by a single locked door.

But when the enemies couldn’t reach him, they waited. And waited. And eventually, the Chevalier grew lonely.

Luckily, he wasn’t entirely alone. He had allies. Huxter and Miss Fanny Bolton were his secret lifelines. When creditors lurked outside, Fanny’s sisters would let out a special cry—like a confused alpine yodel—alerting the garrison. When Fanny and Huxter arrived, they sang the same tune at the door, and the trapdoor opened. Supplies came in: bread, porter, moral support. Strong would emerge, smile, and spend a pleasant evening surrounded by loyal friends.

And then there was the escape route. As previously mentioned, Strong’s kitchen window was close to Costigan’s room next door. A leaden pipe connected the two buildings. One day, Strong joked to Altamont, “If things get bad, I can climb across.” Altamont laughed. “Don’t tell Costigan. He’s got enough duns already.”

But when the siege intensified, Strong used the pipe. One afternoon, he burst into Costigan’s room like a pirate raiding a tea party. “Enemy on the staircase!” he announced, jolly as ever. “I’m taking the scenic route!”

While Marks’s men waited in the hall, Strong dined at the Albion, saw the play, and returned at midnight—much to the astonishment of Mrs. Bolton and Fanny, who hadn’t seen him leave.

For weeks, Strong held out. But morale dipped. Clavering, the supposed partner, remained silent. Letter after letter went unanswered. Not even a five-pound note—which, at the time, would’ve been a fortune—came through. Strong was ready to give up. “I was thinking of ending it all,” he confessed later. “Another week, and I’d have done it.”

Then—miracle of miracles—Altamont dropped from heaven.

“No, not literally,” said Altamont. “I came from Baden-Baden. Had a lucky month.”

“Well, sir,” said Strong, eyes shining, “he paid off every bill. Like a man. Like a gentleman.”

“And I shall buy a bottle of claret for the company,” said Altamont, blushing slightly. “Waiter! A magnum! Let’s drink to good fellows standing by each other. That’s my sentiment, Pendennis—even if I don’t like your name.”

“No?” asked Arthur.

Strong kicked Altamont under the table.

“Why?” asked Arthur.

Altamont, flushed and excited, raised his glass. “Because he was a gentleman. And that’s all that matters. We’re all gentlemen.”

They met at Richmond, where Pendennis joined them for dinner. Both Strong and Altamont were in high spirits—drunk, perhaps, but full of stories. Strong recounted his siege with theatrical flair: the sheriff’s officers’ complaints, Fanny’s signals, Costigan’s horrified reaction when the Chevalier burst in, and finally, Altamont’s divine rescue.

“It’s nothing,” said Altamont modestly. “Just luck. I won at Baden-Baden. I’ve got a system. I’ll make everyone rich. I’ll take Strong with me. He’ll play for me. I trust him more than myself—or Bloundell-Bloundell, or that Italian who ‘stands in.’”

As he emptied his second bottle, Altamont launched into a detailed explanation of his “infallible” gambling system. It involved coolness, capital, and a wheel that never lied. He planned to form a bank, invest everything, and win big. “I’ll put in for Fanny,” he declared suddenly. “She lent Ned two pounds! Didn’t she, Ned?”

“She did,” said Strong proudly.

“Let’s drink to her!”

Arthur pledged the toast with genuine warmth.

Later, Arthur told his uncle about Altamont.

“Altamont?” said the Major. “There’s Lord Westport’s son.”

“No,” said Arthur, laughing. “The guy who came in tipsy to Clavering’s dining room. Said he didn’t like my name, though he thought I was a good fellow.”

“I don’t know any Altamont,” said the Major firmly. “And I suggest you avoid him.”

Arthur chuckled. “He’s quitting the country to make his fortune with a gambling system. He’s partnering with Bloundell, and bringing Strong along as aide-de-camp. What ties Strong and Clavering together, I wonder?”

“Ah,” said the Major, lowering his voice. “I suspect Clavering has secrets. And if he doesn’t, well—that’s a lesson: keep yourself straight, boy. Don’t give anyone power over you.”

Arthur smiled. “But you’ve got power over Clavering, haven’t you? Otherwise, why would he give me that parliamentary seat?”

“Clavering thinks he’s unfit for Parliament,” said the Major. “And he’s right. He could give it to anyone. Why should you be squeamish when the highest nobles do it without blinking?”

Arthur accepted the logic—not because he believed it, but because he wanted to. Because sometimes we justify our choices not because they’re right, but because we like them. And that’s human.

At Tunbridge, Arthur regaled Miss Blanche with the tale of Strong’s imprisonment and Altamont’s heroic rescue. He praised Fanny’s generosity and Altamont’s admiration for her.

Miss Blanche, naturally, was intrigued—and mildly jealous.

Arthur, in their sweet rural drives and moonlit walks, couldn’t resist sharing the story. He described it with his usual satirical twist: how women recover from first loves easily, how emotions shift like fashion trends. Poor Fanny, he said, was proof: she’d loved deeply, suffered terribly, and now was happily engaged to Mr. Huxter—whom Arthur described as “a man of no charm, no wit, no looks, but immense loyalty.”

“You wicked creature,” said Blanche. “Are you jealous of Huxter?”

Arthur’s cheeks flushed—though he denied it.

“Not at all,” he said. “But I wish Fanny had ended up with someone better. I hate stories that end with a man like Huxter. Is life a compromise? Is love a surrender? Should Cupid’s quest end with a man who smells of tobacco and medicine?”

“Perhaps,” said Blanche, “you’re just afraid you’re not enough.”

Arthur sighed. “I don’t think I have much heart. But I made my move at eighteen. I lit my lamp and searched for Cupid. What did I find? A vulgar dancing-woman. I failed. But at least I failed before marriage.”

“Merci du choix,” said Blanche, curtseying.

“Look,” said Arthur, taking her hand, “at least I don’t flatter. I don’t lie. I don’t pretend to be a hero. I don’t claim you’re the most beautiful, the greatest poetess, or the tallest woman in England—like that giantess we saw at the fair.”

“But I think you’re very good-looking,” said Blanche.

“Thank you,” said Arthur.

“I think you sing beautifully. I know you’re clever. I hope you’re kind. And I believe you’ll be companionable.”

And so they sat, under the stars, two people who knew the truth of love: it’s messy, uncertain, and often ends with someone smelling of tobacco. But still, they chose to stay.

CHAPTER LXVI. In which Pen begins his Canvass — Or, How a Young Gentleman Learned to Smile at the World (and Why It Wasn’t as Easy as It Looked)

The great house at Clavering Park had once been a monument of grandeur—before it became a monument of bankruptcy. Now, under Sir Francis Clavering’s stewardship, it was merely a monument of melancholy. The mansion stood like a ghost in its own grounds: most of the rooms shuttered, the corridors echoing with silence, and only a handful of chambers occupied by the Baronet and his two loyal servants from the lodge gate—women who, though not exactly cheerful, were at least experienced in cooking game that had been shot during mornings spent in dreary solitude.

Lightfoot, Sir Francis’s man, had moved on—upward, socially speaking—to become a domestic in Lady Clavering’s service. And according to Mr. Smirke, the man of ceremony who performed the rites, Lightfoot had also performed the even more significant rite of marrying Mrs. Bonner, the elderly lady’s maid, whose heart, it seemed, had been captured not by her years, but by Lightfoot’s youth. Their ambition? To run the Clavering Arms. They would remain in service until quarter-day, when they’d officially take over the inn, complete with dreams of ale, pewter mugs, and the occasional scandalous guest.

Arthur Pendennis, meanwhile, graciously promised he would host his election dinner at the Clavering Arms once Sir Francis vacated his seat. Arthur came down in September, ostensibly for a visit—but really to begin what he called “the canvass.” He arrived just as Sir Francis was preparing to flee from his own home, and within days, Arthur had supplied him with enough ready money to make that escape possible. As soon as the cash was in hand, Sir Francis declared he must attend to business in Chatteris and various nearby watering-places—places known primarily for their racecourses and billiard tables, where "business" usually meant betting and bad decisions.

Arthur, left alone, found himself quite content. He could walk with the gamekeeper in the morning, read in the evening, and enjoy a cigar while scribbling away at his next literary masterpiece. But after three days, he decided he’d rather be anywhere else than in the same room as Sir Francis. So when the Baronet asked for a loan—his usual custom—Arthur handed it over with a smile so wide it might have been carved into stone. It was, in fact, a relief to see Sir Francis go.

But Arthur wasn’t just there to escape loneliness—he was there to win votes. And winning votes required something he hadn’t mastered before: charm. He remembered how unpopular he’d been in Clavering ten years ago—a haughty young dandy who looked down on everyone. Now, he vowed to be different. He would be warm, jovial, accessible. He would be Captain Strong reincarnated.

And so he became Captain Strong.

He laughed at everything, shook hands like a politician handing out free pamphlets, and made appearances at market days and farmers’ dinners as if he’d been born to them. At the Clavering Athenaeum—the Liberal club, established to oppose the aristocratic old reading-room where tradesmen were barred and *Edinburgh Review* copies were considered subversive—he mingled with the opposition preacher, Mr. Simcoe; the two partners of the Chatteris cloth factory; and the Independent minister. He invited the younger partner to dine at the Park, sent hares and partridges to Mrs. Simcoe with a request to read her husband’s latest sermon, and when he felt unwell, he let Mr. Huxter—local apothecary—send medicine and pay a visit. All this was done with such artful ease that even the old gentleman himself might have thought Arthur was genuinely ill.

Old Pendennis would have been proud. Arthur, for his part, found the whole thing deeply amusing. He enjoyed being the kind of man people liked—even if he knew full well he wasn’t one. There was a wicked joy in pretending to be good-natured, when you knew all along you were playing a role.

Yet, on quiet nights, when he walked back from the Athenaeum or from an evening with the Simcoes, something stirred inside him. As he crossed the old bridge over the rushing Brawl, the sound of the water beneath reminded him of childhood. The stars shone bright, the trees whispered secrets, and far off, across the fields, he saw Fairoaks—his mother’s cottage—its windows still lit, as if someone waited there.

He didn’t look directly at it. That light felt like a reproach—like a ghostly eye watching him, warning him. He remembered the room where his mother had sat, praying, worrying, loving him. He remembered the terrace where, as a boy, he had walked untroubled, pure, untouched by the world’s cynicism. Now he was here, pretending, smiling, scheming.

The clock in the town tolled midnight. He turned toward Clavering Avenue, passing under the dark arches of the limes, and entered the park.

The next day, he visited Fairoaks again. This time, the sun was setting. The windows were open. His tenant, Captain Stokes of the Bombay Artillery (whose mother lived in Clavering), greeted him warmly. They toured the grounds, admired the new pond, discussed repairs, and Captain Stokes begged Arthur to name a day when he might do himself and Mrs. Stokes the honor of hosting a dinner.

Arthur apologized for not visiting sooner. “I couldn’t bring myself to,” he said.

“I understand,” said the Captain.

Mrs. Stokes came down in her best dress, children trailing behind her. The youngest clambered over furniture—specifically, the armchair that had once belonged to Arthur’s father. Arthur froze. That chair had been sacred. He wouldn’t have dreamed of sitting in it as a child. Now, a boy was bouncing on it like a trampoline.

“Can Miss Stokes play?” Arthur asked, trying to sound casual.

She played. The piano was old, the notes slightly flat. But Arthur wasn’t listening to the music. He heard his mother singing, saw Laura at the keyboard, and felt the warmth of a past that no longer existed.

Dinner was dull—until the waiter, Hodson, dropped a plate over Arthur’s shoulder. Mr. Hobnell, the local squire, chuckled. “I suppose your hands are slippery with bear’s grease, Hodson. Always dropping crockery, that fellow is—haw, haw!”

Hodson blushed. Arthur burst out laughing. The mood shifted. Soon, the table was alive with jokes. When Arthur suggested Hodson should cut the hare, the Vicar of Tinckleton took up the joke, then the Captains, then Hobnell, arriving late but roaring with laughter. For a moment, it felt like life.

Meanwhile, in London, Blanche Clavering—Lady Clavering’s daughter—had come up from Tunbridge Wells with Mrs. Bonner, her childhood nurse. Mrs. Bonner, about to retire from service and marry Lightfoot, wanted to leave a token of affection. She bought a Bible for Blanche, who insisted on getting a large Johnson’s Dictionary for her mother. Then she splurged on linen for the Clavering Arms and a red-and-yellow neckerchief—for Lightfoot, naturally. Mrs. Bonner adored him—more than a wife, more than a mother. She already imagined him bedecked in jewelry, pins, rings, chains, seals—all purchased at her expense.

They shopped in the Strand. After finishing, Blanche smiled sweetly at a shopman and said, “Pray, sir, will you show us the way to Shepherd’s Inn?”

“Shepherd’s Inn? What on earth for, miss?” Mrs. Bonner asked.

“To see Captain Strong,” Blanche replied. “But I’m not going to see him. I want to visit Fanny Bolton—the girl who nursed Mr. Arthur last year and saved his life. I’ve brought dresses—look!” she added proudly, as if offering charity was a feat of heroism.

Blanche, resplendent in feathers, flowers, silk, and a parasol that probably cost more than Fanny’s entire wardrobe, arrived at Shepherd’s Inn. Mrs. Bolton, scrubbing the floor, stared in awe. Betsy-Jane and Ameliar-Ann gaped.

“My good woman! I wish to see Fanny Bolton—is she here?” Blanche asked.

Mrs. Bolton, suspicious, hesitated. “What do you want with her?”

“I am Lady Clavering’s daughter. You’ve heard of Sir Francis Clavering?”

“Step in, miss. Betsy-Jane, where’s Fanny?”

Fanny was upstairs—in Captain Strong’s rooms.

“In Captain Strong’s rooms!” Blanche exclaimed. “Oh, let’s go there! I know him very well.”

As they climbed, Costigan, a swaggering old gentleman from the court below, muttered to himself: “That’s a devilish foine gyurl, bedad, goan up to Sthrong and Altamont. They’re always having foine gyurlls up their stairs.”

Then—shrieks.

Costigan, thinking a woman was in distress, rushed up. He found the outer door of Strong’s chambers open and began banging the knocker.

After many knocks, the inner door cracked open. A head appeared.

“It’s oi, me boy. Hwhat’s that noise, Sthrong?”

“Go to the d——!” came the reply. The door slammed shut.

Costigan stomped down muttering threats.

But the reader, unlike Costigan, knows the truth.

It had been said that Colonel Altamont was generous. When he had money, he spent it freely. He loved company, especially drinking with others. On this particular day, he had invited Miss Delaval of the Theatre Royal and her mother, Mrs. Hodge, for a river outing. They were to meet at Chambers and walk to a wharf in the Strand.

So when Blanche and Mrs. Bonner arrived at Strong’s door, Grady, Altamont’s servant, welcomed them in. Two bouquets of flowers sat on the table—gifts from Altamont, purchased earlier that morning. Blanche sniffed one, twirled around, looked behind curtains, examined books, prints, and even the plan of the Clavering estate hanging on the wall.

She nearly forgot why she was there.

Grady disappeared into the bedroom with a pair of enormous varnished boots.

“The women’s come,” he said, helping Altamont into them.

“Did you ask ’em if they’d like a drink?” Altamont asked.

Grady returned. “He says, will you take anything to drink?”

Blanche giggled. “Shall we take anything to drink?”

“Well, you may take it or lave it,” Grady grumbled, offended.

“Will we take anything to drink?” Blanche repeated, laughing.

“Grady, my coat!” roared a voice from the bedroom.

“Why, that’s not Mr. Strong’s voice,” Blanche said.

“Grady, my coat!” the voice bellowed again.

Bonner stared, terrified.

The bedroom door opened. A man stepped out—no coat.

He nodded at the women, walked across the room.

“I beg your pardon, ladies. Grady, bring my coat down, sir! Well, my dears, it’s a fine day, and we’ll have a jolly lark at——”

He stopped.

Mrs. Bonner screamed: “Amory! Amory!”

She fainted.

The man rushed to Blanche, seized her, kissed her.

“Yes, Betsy,” he said. “By G—it is me. Mary Bonner knew me. What a fine gal we’ve grown! But it’s a secret, mind. I’m dead, though I’m your father. Your poor mother don’t know it. Kiss me—kiss me close, my Betsy? D—— it, I love you. I’m your old father.”

Blanche shrieked.

The screams echoed through the building.

The man—Amory—rushed to his dressing-case, pulled out a bottle of eau-de-Cologne, and began spraying the fainting women.

Strong appeared from upstairs, saw the scene, and said: “Grady, go wait in the court. If anyone comes—you understand.”

“Is it the play-actress and her mother?” Grady asked.

“Yes—confound you—say there’s nobody in chambers. Party’s off for today.”

“Shall I say that, sir? After I bought them bokays?”

“Yes,” Amory snapped.

Strong reached the door just in time to stop Costigan from entering.

The theatre ladies never made it to Greenwich. Blanche never saw Fanny Bolton. And Costigan, asking Grady what the hell was going on, was told: “It’s another woman. One of them. Women, sir—they’re the cause of most mischief in the world.”

CHAPTER LXVII. In Which Pen Begins to Doubt His Election (And Possibly His Entire Life)

Pen, busy plotting his parliamentary rise like a man who’d just discovered the secret of immortality (or at least a good seat in the House), was suddenly interrupted by news that Lady Rockminster had arrived at Baymouth—along with Laura. The mere mention of Laura’s name sent a shiver down Pen’s spine, not from joy, but from the kind of guilt only a morally flexible gentleman can feel when confronted by someone who still believes in angels.

Laura, his sister—his mother’s legacy, his moral compass, and, as he now realized, possibly his conscience—was coming into town. And what was he doing? Plotting to become a Member of Parliament, marry Blanche Amory (a woman whose charm could be measured in inches of lace and yards of coquetry), and generally behave like a man who’d forgotten how to say “no” to ambition. He felt like a schoolboy caught red-handed stealing tarts, except instead of tarts it was honor, and instead of a headmaster it was Laura—whose pure, unblinking gaze could detect hypocrisy from a mile away.

He wrote her a letter full of poetic flattery, witty banter, and enough sentiment to drown a small country estate. But deep down, he knew he was lying. He was acting like a rogue playing dress-up in a borrowed conscience. Because here was the truth: Laura wasn’t just a sister. She was a mirror. And in her reflection, Pen saw not the polished gentleman he pretended to be, but a man who’d traded sincerity for status, affection for advancement, and peace for power.

Why did a simple girl—kind, innocent, and barely aware of the political machinations of the world—frighten him so? Because she didn’t play games. She didn’t lie. She didn’t pretend. And that made her terrifying. For a man whose life was built on cleverness and compromise, honesty was like an open door to a room full of uncomfortable truths.

As he rode from Clavering to Baymouth, he felt like a criminal heading toward a trial where the judge had never been wrong—and had seen right through him before he even stepped off the horse.

Meanwhile, Laura had spent the past year under Lady Rockminster’s care, learning manners, music, and the fine art of being overlooked in polite society. Poor thing. Though she’d been raised with all the tenderness of a pampered pet, she now found herself in a world where kindness wasn’t always returned. Her mother, Helen, had doted on her so much that no one else seemed to notice her existence. Now, without that protective glow, Laura faced the cold reality of social hierarchy: some people ignored her; others treated her like a charity case; and servants, when they weren’t rude, were at least indifferent.

But Laura? She didn’t complain. Instead, she smiled. And that smile—bright, trusting, utterly genuine—was her superpower. She didn’t need manipulation or flattery. She simply *was*. And because she was, everyone around her softened. Even Lady Rockminster’s long-suffering maid, who had endured forty years of scolding, grew fond of her. When Laura fell ill in Paris, the maid nursed her despite her mistress’s fears of fever. The chef wept when she ate chicken. The footmen celebrated her recovery by getting drunk at a wine shop. Even the most grumpy members of the household became sentimental over her.

And then there was Lady Diana Pynsent—once skeptical, now declaring that Laura was “very agreeable.” A revolution in diplomacy, if ever there was one.

When Pen arrived, Laura came rushing downstairs, perhaps even embracing him in front of Calverley and Coldstream. These two giants, normally as expressionless as statues carved from granite, would have witnessed a severed head rolling down the stairs and shown no more emotion than a pair of garden gnomes. But Laura’s warmth? That was something else entirely.
