She looked better than ever—healthier, brighter, more radiant. Pen couldn’t help but stare. Her eyes sparkled like sunlight on water. Her cheeks blushed with life. And when she kissed him, he felt like he’d been struck by lightning—only this time, it wasn’t pain, it was shame.

“You’re too kind to me, Laura,” he said, voice thick with emotion. “I don’t deserve it.”

“Mamma left you to me,” she replied, brushing his forehead with her lips. “Remember our pact? You come to me when you’re happy—or when you’re sad. So which is it?”

Pen hesitated. Then, weakly: “I’m… trying to be happy.”

“Oh, I know about the Parliament plan!” she said, grinning. “Everyone knows! Lady Rockminster’s doctor talked about it today. I bet it’ll be in the Chatteris paper tomorrow. And Miss Blanche Amory—she’s going to be your wife, isn’t she?”

Pen blinked. “You know about that?”

“Know? All the world does! Tout se sait, as I intend to say to Blanche when I meet her. She wrote me the sweetest letter. Said she adores you. I hope she means it.”

Pen stared. “So you’re not… upset?”

“Upset?” she laughed. “Why should I be? You’re happy. That’s all that matters. And when you become a minister, I’ll wear my best dress and let Blanche present me at court. I’ve been practicing my curtsey.”

And with that, she performed a perfect Parisian bow—complete with dramatic flourish—just as Lady Rockminster entered.

The old lady gave Pen one stiff finger to shake. He bowed awkwardly. She sighed.

“So you’re marrying, are you?” she said.

“Scold him, Lady Rockminster!” Laura chimed in. “He didn’t tell us!”

“I wanted Sir Francis Clavering to give my grandson his seat,” the dowager snapped. “Why didn’t he? I suppose you’re getting rich with this Miss Amory. I wouldn’t take her unless she came with a fortune.”

Pen winced. “Sir Francis is tired of politics. And I… well, I thought it might be worth trying.”

“Ah, yes,” she sneered. “You prefer Miss Amory to my Laura. Well, I’ve no patience with men who do. She’s crooked, I tell you—affected, artificial. After seeing Laura, how could you choose such a creature?”

Pen smiled tightly. “I’m very sorry Miss Amory doesn’t please you.”

“You mean—you don’t care whether I like her or not? Very well. I don’t care either. I won’t marry her. I’m glad I won’t. A little odious thing. When I asked the three attaches at the Embassy why they didn’t fall in love with Laura, they laughed and said they wanted money. Selfish! Cowards! We ran away with each other in my day. No romance left now.”

Pen raised an eyebrow. “Did you consult Laura before offering her to them?”

“Miss Bell has only a little money,” the dowager declared. “She must marry soon. Someone must make a match for her. A girl can’t offer herself.”

“And what if she doesn’t want to be matched?” Pen asked.

“She will,” Lady Rockminster said firmly. “Because I say so.”

Laura returned, smiling. “Have you been asking Arthur why he won’t marry me?” she asked, taking his hand. “He’s going to marry someone else. But I’ll be very fond of her. And I’ll live with them—if he doesn’t keep asking every bachelor who comes to dinner why he won’t marry me.”

Pen exhaled. Relief washed over him. No reproaches. No judgment. Just love. And that made him feel worse.

From then on, he found himself visiting Baymouth constantly. Lady Rockminster welcomed him with the same authority she used to command the household: “Come often. Grandjean cooks well. And you need to learn manners. You’re always thinking about yourself. Don’t blush. It’s natural. My sons were like that until I cured them. Come here. We’ll teach you how to behave. Hecker will pour you wine. On good days, champagne. But no too much. And no disturbing me during my nap.”

She ruled her little kingdom with iron grace, and everyone—servants, guests, even Pen—bowed to her whims.

Her gatherings were modest: the doctor, the vicar, his curate, occasionally a visitor. But Pen found himself drawn to the quiet moments—when Lady Rockminster napped and Laura played the piano. He’d sit in silence, sipping wine, watching her fingers dance across the keys, wondering how anyone could be so pure in a world so corrupt.

One evening, he asked why she hadn’t written when she was ill.

“What good would it have done?” she said gently. “I had Martha and the doctor. You’re too busy to write to women. You have books, newspapers, politics, railroads…”

Pen looked at her. And blushed again. Because he remembered: he hadn’t written. Not once. Not when she was sick. Not when she needed him.

They walked together—through fields, past Fairoaks, along the downs. And as they rode, Pen began to see her clearly: not just as a sister, but as a person—warm, wise, untouched by pretense. In their mother’s lifetime, she’d held back. But now, with no pressure to be anything but herself, she spoke openly, freely, honestly.

They talked about their mother. About her endless love, her silent devotion. Pen learned things he never knew—how she’d visited every cottage, how she’d given food and comfort to those in need, how she’d loved him not for what he could become, but for who he was.

One day, they visited her grave. Pen stood there, silent. Guilty. He’d come to pay respects, but felt unworthy. A man who sold his soul for a seat in Parliament—what right did he have to stand beside a woman who’d lived for love?

They rode past Fairoaks. Pen stopped and asked a boy for a sprig of honeysuckle. The boy handed it over. Pen gave it to Laura. She smiled.

“You weren’t selfish,” she said softly.

“Only a pretty sacrifice,” he muttered.

“But your heart was kind. That’s all that matters.”

Pen sighed. “I wish I could be more like you.”

“You already are,” she said. “You just forget it.”

Later, as they walked home, she asked him what he planned to do in Parliament.

“Women don’t understand politics,” he said, half-joking.

“Then explain it to us,” she insisted. “Lady Diana says Mr. Pynsent spends all day in committees, all night at the House, votes as he’s told, never speaks, and never rises above a subordinate role. Are you going to follow that path?”

“Possibly,” he admitted.

“But why? What’s so brilliant about it?”

“I don’t know,” he said honestly. “Maybe I want to do good. Maybe I want to matter.”

“Then why not stay at home?” she asked. “Write books. Kind books. Books that help people. If fame is vanity, why chase it? If the world is wicked, why stay in it?”

Pen paused.

“And what would you have me do?” he asked.

“I’d have you bring your wife to Fairoaks,” she said. “Live quietly. Study. Help others. Have children who play on the lawn. Pray in our mother’s church. If the world is a temptation, aren’t we told to pray not to be led into it?”

Pen looked at her. And for the first time in years, he didn’t feel like a politician. He felt like a brother. Like a son. Like a man who might actually be capable of choosing love over ambition.

And for a moment, he wondered: what if he did?

Anyone who’s ever passed through the doors of the Wheel of Fortune public-house—famed for hosting Mr. James Morgan’s Club and serving as the scene of Sir Francis Clavering’s dramatic tête-à-tête with Major Pendennis—will know that the place has three rooms on the ground floor, plus the bar where the landlady sits like a queen of minor domestic drama. One room is open to all comers; another is reserved for gentlemen in livery (a class whose dignity is rarely matched by their actual pay); and the third, marked plainly with “Private,” is the exclusive haunt of the “Confidentials,” a select society composed entirely of Messrs. Morgan and Lightfoot.

Now, Mr. Morgan, that quiet, observant soul who could listen to a conversation without so much as twitching an eyelash, had overheard a rather juicy exchange between Strong and Major Pendennis at the latter’s lodgings. He’d filed it away like a rare stamp or a secret recipe for success. And when Pendennis later visited the Wheel of Fortune, Morgan followed like a shadow, slipping into the Confidential room just as the two gentlemen began their argument in the parlour next door. From a certain corner—a spot that must have been designed by fate itself—Morgan could hear nearly every word, and what he heard confirmed his darkest suspicions: the whole affair was bigger than he’d imagined.

“Ah, so he recognized Altamont right off in Sydney?” Morgan muttered to himself, sipping brandy-and-water like a man with secrets. “Clavering ain’t married to Lady C. any more than I am! Altamont’s the real father—Altamont’s a convict! Harthur gets into Parliament, and the Governor promises not to split. By Jove, what a crafty old fox that governor is!” A grin spread across Morgan’s face, as if he’d just won the lottery while still wearing his master’s boots. “If only I weren’t so old and burdened with society’s ridiculous prejudices,” he said aloud, staring into the mirror, “I might marry her myself. Dammy, James Morgan, you could be Lord Blanche.”

But since marriage wasn’t in the cards, Morgan decided to play the next best hand: blackmail. With information this explosive, he could make money from almost everyone involved. Sir Francis Clavering? He’d keep quiet about the scandal. Colonel Altamont? He’d be terrified of exposure. Young Harthur? The lad would pay handsomely to avoid being revealed as a convict’s son—and worse, as someone who got his seat in Parliament through shady deals. As for Lady C., well, if she’s tired of Clavering, she’ll pay to get rid of him. If she’s worried about her son, she’ll pay to protect him. And Miss Blanche? She’ll pay to reclaim her rightful inheritance. “Dammy,” Morgan concluded, “with cards like these, James Morgan, you’re practically a millionaire. It’s practically a miracle. Everyone will subscribe. And with what I’ve already made, I can quit this job, give my old boss notice, become a gentleman myself, and even hire a servant—begad!”

All this while, Morgan remained calm, courteous, and utterly professional. He polished boots, curled hair, brought tea, and bore the Major’s grumblings and senile sarcasm with the patience of a saint—or perhaps a man who knew he was about to become richer than his employer.

One key player in this tangled web, Colonel Altamont, was conveniently absent from London when Morgan learned the truth. The valet, knowing Clavering’s favorite haunt—the Shepherd’s Inn—hurried there after the conversation ended. But the bird had flown. Altamont had collected his Derby winnings and fled to the Continent. “He’ll blow it all at the gambling dens along the Rhine,” Morgan sighed. “And I could’ve had a nice chunk of it. How annoying that he couldn’t wait a few days!” Yet Morgan bore disappointment with the same equable face he wore when polishing boots. Until the moment came, he served faithfully: boots shined, hair curled, tea delivered, insults swallowed with silent dignity. Who would guess, watching him pack trunks or assist at dinner in country houses, that Morgan was wealthier than his master, knew more secrets than the Prime Minister, and had turned the landlady into his personal slave?

In fact, Mrs. Brixham was under Morgan’s thumb. He owned the lease of the house she rented, held her name and her son’s on various bills, and even had a bill of sale that made her furniture his property. Her son worked in an insurance office—where Morgan could send him to “quod” (prison) with a single note. She was a clergyman’s widow, and Morgan took pleasure in making her fetch his bootjack and slippers. Her entire life—her son’s black profile, her daughter’s picture, the very image of Tiddlecot Church where she’d been married—was now part of Morgan’s collection, hanging in his back-parlour like trophies.

On this particular evening, Morgan sat in the ex-curate’s horse-hair chair, drinking brandy-and-water bought with the landlady’s own money, which only made him drink more freely. He’d eaten supper and was on his third tumbler when the Major returned from the Club, weary and foul-tempered.

Morgan swore violently at the bell and finished his drink before ascending. The Major, already annoyed, greeted him with a torrent of abuse. Morgan endured it silently, kneeling to remove the boots with perfect obedience—though below, beneath the surface, he growled like a caged bear.

When it came time to take off the coat, Morgan had to stand close enough for the Major to smell the brandy on his breath. “You reek of gin and bad decisions,” the Major snapped.

That was the final straw. Morgan exploded. “Drunk, am I? A beast? D——d? You infernal old miscreant! Shall I wring your neck? Drown you in that pail of water? Do you think I’m some dog who’ll take your insolence? I’ve waited on you for twelve years, and you’ve cursed me every day! You call me names, but I’ve seen plenty of gentlemen drunker than I am. And if I choose to drink, why shouldn’t I?”

The Major grabbed a knife from the table. “If you take one step forward, I’ll stab you. Go downstairs, you drunken brute. Send for your book and wages tomorrow. Never show your face here again. Your impudence has gone too far. You’ve grown rich, and rich men don’t serve. Get out.”

“And where should I go, pray?” Morgan retorted, sitting down calmly. “Isn’t tomorrow morning convenient enough? Tootyfay mame shose, sivvaplay, munseer?”

“Silence, you beast, and leave!”

Morgan laughed—a dry, chilling laugh. “Look here, Pendennis. Since I’ve been in this room, you’ve called me beast, brute, dog, and d——d. How do you think a man likes that? Twelve years I’ve served you. Twelve years of curses and insults. Do you think I’m a dog? That I can’t talk back? I’m not going to take it anymore. I’m quitting. I’ve combed your wig, buckled your girths, and tended your sick body long enough. Don’t look at me like that—I’m sitting in my own chair, in my own room, telling the truth. I won’t be your beast anymore. I’m James Morgan, and I’m done.”

The Major, stunned by the sudden rebellion, felt his anger cool like a fever breaking. He found himself oddly impressed—like a fencer admiring a bold opponent who lands a clean blow. “You are no longer my servant,” he said, regaining composure. “This house may be yours, but these lodgings are mine. I shall move out tomorrow. For now, I wish to sleep. Leave me.”

“We’ll settle things,” Morgan said, rising. “I’m not done with you. Or your family. Or the Claverings. You’ll see.”

“Leave the room, sir. I’m tired.”

“Hah!” Morgan sneered. “You’ll be more tired of me before it’s over.” And with that, he walked out, leaving the Major alone with his thoughts.

The old gentleman sat by the fire, reflecting on servants, ingratitude, and the difficulty of replacing a man who knew how to make mutton broth and polish boots better than anyone else. He missed Morgan’s boot-varnish—it was *incomparable*. He remembered how Morgan had cared for him when he was ill. “Gad, it’s hard to lose such a fellow,” he muttered. “But he must go. He’s grown rich. And rich men don’t stay loyal.”

He thought of the shower-bath, which had helped soothe his nerves. The umbrella incident, the paint-smell at the Club—they all faded under the weight of this new drama. “Confound the insolent villain,” he whispered. “He was the best servant in England.”

Just then, a timid knock. The door opened. Mrs. Brixham stood there, pale and trembling.

“God bless my soul, Mrs. Brixham!” the Major gasped. “It’s late. What is it?”

“I wish to speak to you, sir,” she said, voice quivering. “About Morgan.”

“Ah. Of course. He’s cooled off at the pump. Can’t take him back. I’d decided earlier—he’s involved in discount schemes. I suppose you’ve heard?”

“Oh, sir,” she cried, “I know. I borrowed from him five years ago. I’ve paid him back many times over. But he owns everything. My house, my furniture, my son’s future. He’s ruined me. He’s a dreadful man.”

The Major blinked. “Eh? Tout pis. Dev’lish sorry for you. And I must leave your house after lodging here so long. There’s nothing to be done. I must go.”

And so, with a sigh, the Major prepared for bed, leaving behind not just a servant—but a world turned upside down.

The next morning dawned with all the solemnity of a court trial—except that the only judge present was a man who’d once commanded troops in India and now found himself locked in a battle of wits with his own valet.

Pendennis’s shutters were drawn back by Morgan, who arrived as usual—dressed like a man who’d just walked out of a portrait painted by someone who didn’t like him much. He carried clothes, water cans, and a silver dressing case so polished it could’ve been used to start a fire. The old major lay in bed, half-awake, half-irritated, like a lion who’d just realized he’d been served breakfast by a monkey.

“It’s you, is it?” he grumbled, squinting at Morgan through the haze of early morning and bad decisions. “I shan’t take you back again, you understand.”

“I ain’t the least bit interested in being taken back,” Morgan replied, straightening his cravat with the dignity of a man about to deliver a eulogy at his own funeral. “Nor serving you nor any man. But seeing as you’re still in my house, and I want you to be comfortable—until you leave—I thought I’d come up and do what’s necessary.”

And so, for the last time, Mr. James Morgan laid out the razor, adjusted the comb, and placed the toothbrush where only a gentleman would expect it—next to the soap, not under the sink. Then, with a gravity that might have made a bishop blush, he turned to the major.

“Thinking you might need a respectable fellow until you sorted yourself out,” Morgan said, “I spoke to a young man last night. He’s here.”

“Indeed,” said the major, from beneath his sheets like a king inspecting a new servant. “And who might this paragon be?”

“He’s been in the first families,” Morgan declared, puffing slightly. “I can vouch for his respectability. Lord De la Pole gave him to his nephew, young Lord Cubley. He went abroad with him, but refused to go to Scotland—his chest is delicate, you see—and so he’s free to serve you or not, as you please.”

“You’re monstrously polite,” grinned the major, clearly enjoying the absurdity of the situation.

And indeed, Morgan had gone to his club—the Wheel of Fortune—after the previous night’s drama, found Frosch (a courier and valet fresh off a foreign tour), and told him: “I’ve had a devil of a blow-up with my employer. I’m retiring from the business altogether. If you want a temporary job, apply at Bury Street.” And thus, Frosch was born into the Pendennis household.

“Very polite,” said the major, nodding. “Your recommendation will carry immense weight.”

Morgan blushed. He knew the major was teasing him—‘a-chaffin’ of him,’ as he put it in his head. But he stood tall. “He has served you before, sir,” he said proudly. “Lord De la Pole gave him to his nephew. He’s been on tour. He doesn’t wish to go to Fitzurse Castle—cold weather, delicate chest. So he’s available.”

“Ah,” said the major, “you’re quite the diplomat. Come in, Frosch—you’ll do very well. Mr. Morgan, would you kindly—”

“I shall show him what’s necessary,” Morgan interrupted, already stepping forward. “What’s customary for you to have done. Will you take breakfast here or at the Club, Major Pendennis?”

“Here,” said the major. “Then we’ll make our arrangements afterward.”

“If you please, sir.”

“And now,” said the major, “would you oblige me by leaving the room?”

Morgan withdrew, seething. The major’s politeness was worse than insults. It was like being insulted by a statue.

While the old gentleman performed his mysterious toilette—applying cologne like a general preparing for battle—we, too, shall retire with modesty and discretion.

After breakfast, the major and his new aide-de-camp began packing. The bachelor’s life was simple. No unnecessary wardrobe. Just a Bible (his mother’s), a road book, Pen’s novel (bound in calf, because elegance matters), the Duke of Wellington’s despatches, and portraits of generals, sovereigns, and various other important people—mostly men who’d never met him but looked stern enough to impress guests.

His belongings fit neatly into the same boxes he’d brought fifteen years earlier. A maid named Betty—known as “Slavey” to Morgan—brought them down and dusted them with the reverence of someone cleaning a sacred relic.

Morgan watched her every move. He hadn’t spoken to Mrs. Brixham since the previous night’s threats, but his silence was louder than any shout. She trembled in the corner, waiting for the axe to fall.

Meanwhile, the major, cane in hand, supervised the packing. Papers were burned. Closets emptied. Doors flung open like they were confessing sins. All boxes closed—except one: the desk.

That’s when Morgan returned, books in hand.

“Since I wish to speak to you privately,” he said, “perhaps you’d ask Frosch to step downstairs?”

“Bring two cabs, Frosch,” said the major. “Wait downstairs until I ring.”

Frosch obeyed. Morgan watched him walk away, then produced his accounts.

Simple. Clear. Settled in minutes.

“And now,” said Morgan, pocketing the cheque and signing his name with a flourish, “now that our accounts are closed, I propose to speak to you as one man to another.”

Morgan liked the sound of his own voice. He often practiced speeches at the club or in the kitchen.

“I possess valuable information,” he announced.

“Of what nature?” asked the major.

“A marriage that isn’t a marriage,” Morgan said. “A baronet who isn’t married at all. His wife? Married to someone else. You know it, sir. I know it.”

The major understood instantly.

“Oh,” he said, raising an eyebrow. “So this is why you were listening at the door last night. I forgot to check the keyhole. Should’ve known there was a spy behind it.”

“I may have my schemes,” Morgan replied. “Just like you may have yours. I get information. I act on it. I profit from it. A poor servant can have luck, too. Don’t you try to play the aristocrat with me. That’s all gammon. I’m English. I’m as good as you.”

“To what the devil does this tend?” the major asked, standing up. “How does this concern me?”

“How does it concern *me*?” Morgan shot back. “How does it concern your nephew? His seat in Parliament? Bigamy charges? How does it concern *that*? Why should you be the only one with secrets? Why shouldn’t I get a cut? I found it out too. Make it worth my while, and I’ll keep quiet. Let Arthur take his seat. Let him marry the rich widow. I don’t want her. But I want my share. Five hundred a year. Easy. Pay me the first quarter now, and I’m as silent as a mouse. There’s your chequebook.”

“And there’s this,” said the major, pointing to a small double-barrelled pistol on his desk.

It belonged to the Indian commander-in-chief, a man who’d led armies and survived battles. It had seen action. It had killed men. It had also, apparently, sat in a drawer for fifteen years.

“One more word,” said the major, “and I’ll shoot you like a mad dog. Stop—by Jove, I’ll do it now. You’ll assault me? Strike an old man? Kneel down and say your prayers, sir. By God, you shall die.”

Morgan stared. For a moment, he looked terrified. Then—he shrieked.

“Murder! Police!”

To everyone’s surprise, the major wheeled the table aside, walked to the other window, and beckoned the policeman passing by.

“Come up here, policeman,” he said calmly. Then he turned to Morgan.

“You miserable sneak,” he said. “That pistol hasn’t been loaded in fifteen years. You’d have known that if you weren’t such a coward.”

The policeman entered, followed by Mrs. Brixham and her maid, who had been trying to close the door against the curious crowd gathering outside.

“I’ve discharged this drunken scoundrel,” the major announced. “He insulted and assaulted me. I took up a pistol. As you can see, it’s unloaded. This coward screamed before he was hurt. I’m charging him with theft. I want his trunks searched.”

“No!” roared Morgan. “You gave me those things! You gave them to me!”

The major laughed.

“We’ll see,” he said.

And the guilty valet remembered the lawn-fronted shirts, the gold-headed cane, the opera-glass—things he’d assumed ownership of, assuming no one would notice.

The policeman examined the trunks. Inside were cigars of dubious origin, jewels of even more dubious quality, and French prints that would’ve made any decent man blush.

Morgan looked like a man who’d just discovered his soul was on sale.

“He’ll smash me,” thought the major. “But I’ll crush him now.”

But then he saw Mrs. Brixham’s face—fearful, pleading. And he hesitated.

A man in prison might talk. And some secrets were better left buried.

“Stop,” he said. “Policeman. I’ll speak with him alone.”

“Do you give Mr. Morgan in charge?” asked the officer.

“I haven’t brought a charge yet,” the major replied, giving Morgan a look that said, *You’re not getting out of this.*

“Thank you, sir,” whispered Morgan.

“Go outside and wait,” the major said. “Now, Morgan. You played one game with me. And you lost. Though you had the best hand. But you’ve got to pay.”

“Yes, sir,” Morgan muttered.

“I only found out last week,” the major continued, “what kind of game you’ve been running. Young De Boots, of the Blues, recognized you. The man who came to barracks, doing business—one-third money, one-third eau-de-Cologne, one-third French prints. You confounded demure old sinner! I didn’t care what you took. But I took my shot—and hit the bullseye.”

“What do you want?” Morgan asked.

“My bills,” said the major. “In your leather pocket-book. You’ll burn Mrs. Brixham’s bill?”

“No!” growled Morgan. “I won’t part with my property.”

“You lent her sixty pounds five years ago,” said the major. “She’s paid you fifty pounds a year ever since. You have a bill of sale on her furniture and a note for a hundred and fifty pounds. She told me last night. You’ve bled that poor woman dry.”

“I won’t give it up,” Morgan insisted.

“Policeman!” called the major.

“You shall have the bill,” Morgan said, defeated.

“You’re not going to take money from me, and you a gentleman?” the major said.

“I shall want you directly,” he told the policeman, who left and returned shortly.

“No, my good sir,” the major said, “I have no desire for further financial dealings. But let us draw up a little paper. You’ll sign it. No—wait. You’ll write it. You’ve improved your handwriting lately. Sit down. Write: ‘Bury Street, St. James’s, October 21, 18—.’”

Morgan wrote.

“‘I, James Morgan, having come into the service of Arthur Pendennis, Esquire, acknowledge receipt of liberal wages and board for fifteen years.’ You can’t object to that.”

“During fifteen years,” Morgan wrote.

“‘In which time, by my own care and prudence, I have amassed sufficient funds to purchase the house in which my master resides, and to effect other savings. Among others, I may mention my tenant, Mrs. Brixham, who, in consideration of sixty pounds advanced five years ago, has paid me two hundred and fifty pounds, and given me a note of hand for one hundred and twenty pounds, which I hereby restore to her at the request of my late master, Major Arthur Pendennis, and thereby release her furniture from the bill of sale.’ Have you written?”

“I’m d——d if I sign it,” Morgan said.

“Too bad,” said the major. “It will happen whether you sign or not.”

“Let us continue,” he said. “‘And having, in return for my master’s kindness, stolen his property—now upstairs in my trunks—and having uttered falsehoods regarding his and other honourable families, I hereby express regret for these actions, declare myself unworthy of belief, and hope to amend my ways in the future. Signed, James Morgan.’”

“I won’t sign it,” Morgan said.

“My good man,” the major chuckled, “it will happen anyway. I won’t use this unless forced. Mrs. Brixham and the policeman will witness it. I’ll return her note. And you’ll confirm she’s quit of you.”

Frosch returned with the cab. The boxes were packed. The major left.

Mrs. Brixham, once Morgan was gone, rushed to thank him, tears in her eyes, a speech ready on her lips.

The major shook her hand, cut her off mid-sentence, and walked out.

From the parlour window, Morgan watched him go, spitting curses like a man who’d just lost his empire.

But the major wasn’t afraid. He strutted away with his cane, looking like a man who’d just won a war he didn’t know he was fighting.

Hours later, as Morgan sat on the doorstep, smoking a cigar (a treasonous act in the presence of the major), Arthur Pendennis arrived.

“My uncle out, I suppose, Morgan?” he asked.

“Major Pendennis is out, sir,” Morgan replied, bowing without touching his cap. “He left today. I no longer have the honour of serving him.”

“Indeed. Where is he?”

“I believe he’s taken temporary lodgings at Cox’s Hotel, in Jermyn Street.”

Pause.

“Are you in town long, sir? Are you in chambers? I should like the honour of calling on you. Perhaps you’d grant me a quarter of an hour?”

Arthur raised an eyebrow. “Do you want my uncle to take you back?”

“I want no such thing,” Morgan snapped. “I’d see him—”

He stopped. Glared. Then softened.

“No, sir. Thank you. I only wish to speak with you. On business. Perhaps you’d walk into my house?”

“If it’s only a minute or two,” said Arthur, thinking, *He wants me to patronize him.* “I’ll listen.”

And so, he entered.

A card already hung in the window: **APARTMENTS TO LET.**

Morgan led him to the dining room, offered a chair, sat down, and began to speak.

Of course, the reader already knows what he said.

CHAPTER LXX. In Which Pendennis Counts His Eggs (And Finds Them Rather Few)

Our hero, Arthur Pendennis, had just arrived in London—though only for a fleeting visit, like a pigeon who drops in to check if the bread’s still warm before flying off again. After depositing his fellow travelers at a hotel (where they were presumably left to fend for themselves), he hurried off to Lamb Court, where his chambers basked in whatever sunlight dared venture into that grim yet oddly cozy corner of legal existence. In chambers, freedom is the substitute for sunshine; and while Temple dwellers grumble about damp walls and bad coffee, they secretly enjoy their quiet, solitary reigns over cramped rooms filled with law books, pipe smoke, and the occasional ghost of ambition.

Pen’s domestic announced that Warrington was already there—news which sent Arthur bounding up the stairs as if he’d been shot from a cannon. He found George, as usual, buried beneath a mountain of newspapers and reviews, the air thick with the comforting aroma of tobacco and intellectual neglect. The two greeted each other with the kind of rough-hewn warmth that only Englishmen can muster: no hugs, no tears, just a firm handshake and a muttered “Well, young one?” followed by “How are you, old boy?”—a greeting so profoundly unemotional it could pass for indifference, yet packed with more affection than a Frenchman’s entire emotional vocabulary.

Alphonse and Frederic would have wept, embraced, and recited poetry about each other’s noble hearts. Max and Wilhelm would have kissed each other on the cheek, then lit cigars and debated whether the war in the Balkans was really worth it. But not our Britons. They nod. They smile. They say “How-d’you-do?” after having saved each other from drowning in a river or being eaten by wolves. Then tomorrow, they’ll barely acknowledge each other at breakfast, merely exchanging glances and muttering, “Ah, yes. You’re here.” It’s a mystery wrapped in a stiff upper lip, this thing called English friendship.

“Been in Switzerland?” asked Pen, settling into a chair.

“Yes,” said Warrington, puffing thoughtfully. “Couldn’t find decent tobacco anywhere until Strasbourg, where I got some caporal. That’s when I knew I was back in civilization.”

One might imagine he’d seen the Matterhorn, heard the Alps sing, stood transfixed by glaciers and lakes—but no. He’d rather talk about tobacco. The grandeur of nature? Too much to handle. Instead, he’d let it seep into his writing later, like a well-placed metaphor in an essay on public sanitation.

He gave a brief, almost reluctant account of his travels: Switzerland, northern Italy, the Tyrol—then Vienna, Dresden, and the Rhine. He spoke of them in a tone as if he’d been forced to endure them under duress. As if the view of the Rhine had made him melancholy. As if the Alps had inspired nothing but regret.

Meanwhile, Pen offered his own travelogue: country life—boring. Canvassing—slower than a snail on a Sunday. Now in town for a few days, heading next to Tunbridge Wells, where friends would be *uncommonly slow*, too. How difficult it is to get an Englishman to admit he’s enjoyed himself!

“And the seat in Parliament?” Warrington asked, eyes gleaming. “All settled?”

“All right,” Pen replied. “As soon as Parliament meets and a new writ comes out, Clavering retires, and I step into his shoes. Like slipping into a pair of inherited slippers—only less comfortable.”

“Under which king does Bezonian speak or die?” Warrington inquired, quoting some obscure political aphorism.

“Do we come out as Liberal Conservative, Government man, or on our own hook?”

“Hem,” said Pen. “No politics now. Everyone’s politics are pretty much the same. I don’t have enough land to be a Protectionist—nor would I be one even if I owned half the county. I’ll go mostly with the Government, but ahead of them on social issues—don’t grin, you old cynic—I’ve been reading the Blue Books. I’m going to come out strong on sanitation and colonization. Not because I’m a reformer, mind you, but because it’s fashionable.”

“We reserve the right to vote against the Government,” Warrington added, “though we usually don’t. We’re friends of the people avant tout. We give lectures at the Clavering Institute, shake hands with mechanics who’ve read the Times, and think the franchise should be expanded—though we’re always ready to accept office once the House has listened to a few crack speeches and the Administration sees our merit.”

“I’m not Moses,” Pen said, with a touch of sorrow in his voice. “I have no laws from Heaven. I don’t belong to the mountain. My faith isn’t strong enough to lead men. Nor my vanity, nor my hypocrisy. I won’t tell lies, George—but I’ll go along with those that are necessary and widely accepted. Give a man the benefit of his skepticism. If I find something good to say in Parliament, I’ll say it. If there’s a fair measure, I’ll support it. If a decent job opens up, I’ll take it and be grateful. But I wouldn’t flatter a great man any more than a mob. And now you know as much about my politics as I do. What call have I to be a Whig? Whiggism isn’t divine. Why not vote with the Liberal Conservatives? They’ve done what the Whigs never would without them. Who converted both? The Radicals—and the country outside. I think the Morning Post is often right, and Punch is often wrong. I don’t profess a call—I just take advantage of a chance. Parlons d’autre chose.”

“The next thing at your heart, after ambition, is love, I suppose?” Warrington said, leaning back. “How are your young loves doing? Are we going to change our condition and give up these chambers? Are you going to divorce me, Arthur, and marry someone else?”

“I suppose so,” said Pen. “She’s very good-natured and lively. She sings. She doesn’t mind smoking. She’ll have a fair fortune—I don’t know how much—but my uncle says the Begum will be generous. And I think Blanche is dev’lish fond of me,” he added, sighing.

“That means we accept her caresses and her money,” Warrington observed dryly.

“Haven’t we said before that life is a transaction?” Pendennis replied.

“I don’t pretend to break my heart about her. I’ve told her plainly what I feel—and I’ve engaged myself to her. Since I last saw her, especially during the past two months in the country, she’s grown fonder and fonder. Her letters—especially to Laura—show it. Mine have been simple: no raptures, no vows—just treating it as an affaire faite. Not eager to hurry or delay.”

“And Laura?” Warrington asked quietly.

“Laura, George,” said Pen, looking his friend straight in the eye—“by heaven, Laura is the best, noblest, dearest girl the sun ever shone upon.”

His voice dropped. He seemed unable to finish. He reached out his hand. Warrington took it and nodded.

“Have you only found that out now, young un?” Warrington asked after a pause.

“Who hasn’t learned things too late, George?” Arthur cried, his emotions rising. “Whose life isn’t a disappointment? Who carries his heart whole to the grave without losing a piece? I’ve never known anyone truly happy. Everyone has to pay some price to Fate. Lucky if we’re left alone afterward, once we’ve paid our fine. Suppose I’ve just realized I’ve lost the greatest prize in the world—now that it can’t be mine? That for years I had an angel under my tent and let her go? Am I the only one? Ah, dear old boy, am I the only one? And do you think my lot is easier because I admit I deserve it?”

“She’s gone from us,” he whispered. “God bless her.”

“She was in this room once,” said George. “I saw her. Heard her sweet low voice. Saw her smile—those eyes shining so kindly. A face remembered in night watches—always blessed, always loved—gone now.”

A glass with a nosegay. A Bible with Helen’s handwriting. All that remained of a brief, brilliant flower of his life.

Say it’s a dream. Say it passes. Better the memory of a dream than waking from a blank stupor.

They sat in silence, each lost in thought, aware of the other’s pain.

Eventually, Pen broke the quiet. “I must go see my uncle and report progress.”

The Major had written in a foul mood. He was getting old.

“I should like to see you in Parliament, snugly settled with a comfortable house and an heir to the name before I make my bow,” the letter read. “Show me these, and then let old Arthur Pendennis make room for the younger fellows. He’s walked the Pall Mall pave long enough.”

“There’s a kindness about the old heathen,” Warrington remarked. “He cares for more than himself—at least for part of himself not buttoned into his coat. For you and your race. He wants the Pendennises to multiply and inherit the land. The old patriarch blesses you from Bays’s Club window, then gets carried off and buried under the flags of St. James’s Church, in sight of Piccadilly and the cabstand. An edifying ending.”

“The new blood I bring into the family,” mused Pen, “is rather tainted. If I’d chosen, I wouldn’t have picked Amory as progenitor. Or Snell. Or our Oriental ancestors. By the way—who was Amory?”

Amory was lieutenant of an Indiaman. Blanche wrote verses about him—the storm, the mountain wave, the seaman’s grave, the gallant father. Amory drowned commanding a ship between Calcutta and Sydney. He and the Begum weren’t happy together. She’s been unlucky in husbands, the poor lady. Between ourselves, a more despicable creature than Sir Francis Clavering, Baronet of Clavering Park, never—

“Never legislated for his country,” Warrington interrupted.

Pen blushed.

“At Baden,” Warrington continued, “I found Strong in great state, wearing all his orders. He told me he quarrelled with Clavering—had almost as bad an opinion of him as you do. In fact, I think he confided that Clavering was an utter scoundrel. That fellow Bloundell, who taught you card-playing at Oxbridge, was with Strong. Time has polished him—made him a more accomplished rascal than he was in college. But the king of the place was Colonel Altamont—carrying all before him, giving flies to the whole society, breaking the bank, they said.”

“My uncle knows something about Altamont,” Pen said. “Clavering knows something too. There’s something louche about him. But come! I must go to Bury Street, like a dutiful nephew.”

Taking his hat, he prepared to leave.

“I’ll walk with you,” said Warrington.

They descended the stairs, pausing at Pen’s chambers—which, as the reader knows, were now on the lower floor. Here, Pen began dabbing himself with eau-de-Cologne, carefully scenting his hair and whiskers.

“What’s wrong?” growled Warrington. “You haven’t been smoking. Is it my pipe that poisoned you?”

“I’m going to call on some women,” Pen said. “Dining with them. They’re passing through town, staying at an hotel in Jermyn Street.”

Warrington watched with amused interest as Pen transformed himself into a walking advertisement for elegance—gorgeous shirt-front, fresh gloves, glistening boots. George, meanwhile, wore thick high-lows, his shirt torn at the breast, collar ragged from his blue beard.

“Well, young un,” he said simply, “I like you to be a buck. Somehow. When I walk with you, it’s like having a rose in my button-hole. And you’re still affable. I don’t think there’s another young fellow in the Temple who turns out like you. And I don’t believe you’ve ever been ashamed of walking with me.”

“Don’t laugh at me, George,” Pen said.

“I say, Pen,” Warrington continued, sadly, “if you write—if you write to Laura, I wish you’d say ‘God bless her’ from me.”

Pen blushed. Then looked at Warrington. Then burst into uncontrollable laughter.

“I’m going to dine with her!” he exclaimed. “I brought her and Lady Rockminster up from the country today—made it two days—slept last night at Bath. Come and dine, George! I may ask anyone I please, and the old lady keeps talking about you.”

George hesitated. George had an article to write. George wavered. And oh, strange to say—agreed.

They decided to call on the ladies first, then march off to dinner in high spirits.

Once more, the dear face shone upon him. Once more, the sweet voice spoke. Once more, the tender hand pressed a welcome.

Half an hour before dinner, old Lady Rockminster declared: “You will go and see your uncle now, Mr. Pendennis. You won’t bring him to dinner—no. His old stories are intolerable. I want to talk to Mr. Warrington. I daresay he’ll amuse us. We’ve heard all your stories. We’ve been together for two whole days, and I think we’re getting tired of each other.”

So, obeying her ladyship’s command, Arthur went downstairs and walked to his uncle’s lodgings.

The dinner was served just as Arthur burst in, looking like a man who’d been chased by a bear and lost the race. Lady Rockminster, ever the queen of punctuality, began to scold him for his tardiness—until Laura, peering at her cousin with the sharp eye of a hawk trained on emotional distress, noticed that Arthur’s face was paler than a ghost’s handkerchief and his eyes were wide enough to swallow a small village.

“Arthur!” she exclaimed, cutting off the old lady mid-rant. “Are you ill? What has happened?”

Arthur gulped down a whole glass of sherry like it was a potion to cure existential dread. “I’ve heard something extraordinary,” he said, glancing nervously at the servants. “But I’ll tell you later.”

He spent the rest of the meal twitching under the table like a squirrel trying to hide from a cat. Lady Rockminster, meanwhile, was losing patience faster than a teapot loses steam.

“Stop stamping your feet!” she snapped. “You’ve trodden on Fido, and knocked over his saucer. Look at Mr. Warrington—he keeps his boots still, as if he were made of marble!”

At dessert—the eternal torture of dinners that never end—Lady Rockminster sighed dramatically. “This has been the most tedious dinner in history. I suppose something’s happened, and you’re all huddled together plotting secrets. Well, I’m going to bed. No tea for me tonight. Good night, Mr. Warrington. Do come again—when there’s no business to discuss.”

With that, she swept out of the room like a storm cloud retreating after a thunderclap. George and the others followed, but Warrington paused, about to bid farewell to Laura, when Arthur stopped him with a hand like a vice.

“Wait, George,” Arthur said. “You must hear this too. It’s urgent. I need your advice.”

Laura’s heart skipped a beat. Her cheeks flared up like sunrise over a volcano. “It’s about Blanche?” she whispered, barely audible.

“Yes,” Arthur replied, “and it’s the most unbelievable story.” He leaned forward, lowering his voice. “When I left you to go to my uncle’s lodgings, I found Morgan—the servant who’s been with him for years—at the door. He told me my uncle had left the house earlier that day, gone to an hotel. I came in, asked for him—gone out to dinner. Then Morgan said he had something *very* important to tell me. So I went into the house—now *his* house, apparently—and he dropped the bombshell: Blanche’s father is not dead. He’s alive. And the marriage between Clavering and the Begum? Not a marriage at all.”

Warrington blinked. “So Blanche inherits everything?”

“Possibly,” Arthur said. “But let’s think about who her father is. Amory—Clavering knows it, my uncle knows it—is an escaped convict. He wasn’t drowned in New South Wales, as Blanche thought. He was a prisoner, not a ship captain. Lady Clavering told me they weren’t happy. She said she’d tell me everything one day. And once, she wept and said it was hard to admit she was glad her husband was dead. Twice in her life, she said, she’d chosen badly.”

A long silence fell. The candle flames flickered like nervous witnesses.

“And now,” Arthur continued, “this convict has returned. But he can’t come forward. If he does, he’ll be caught and sent back to the hulks. But he’s been holding this secret over Clavering’s head—extorting money from him for years. And now, Morgan—who knows everything—has decided to sell the secret to *us*. He even asked me for an annuity to keep quiet. Said I was trafficking with the Begum’s misfortune, and would extort a seat in Parliament from Clavering. As if I’d stoop to such treachery!”

He looked furious enough to start a war. “My uncle—my own flesh and blood—was involved in this! Trading on a woman’s misery for political gain! Can you imagine? Our mother’s son—me—dealing in such dishonour?”

Laura clutched his hand. “Oh, Arthur,” she said softly. “I can’t believe it.”

Warrington cleared his throat. His voice trembled. “No,” he said. “Arthur Pendennis cannot marry a convict’s daughter and sit in Parliament as a member for the hulks. You must break it off. No explanations. Just say family reasons make it impossible. Better that those poor women think you’re faithless than know the truth. And I’ll get Clavering to sign a letter acknowledging your decision. It’s better than being tied to a lie.”

He didn’t dare look at Laura. He knew what he was saying was the end of his own hopes. The wave of loss crashed over him. He buried his face in his hands.

Arthur stood up suddenly, eyes locked on Warrington. Then he turned away.

Laura rose, walked to Arthur, took his hand, and kissed it. “She thinks so too,” George murmured. “God bless her.”

“Her father’s shame isn’t Blanche’s fault,” Laura said, pale but firm. “If you were married, would you leave her because she did nothing wrong? Are you not pledged to her? Would you abandon her in misfortune? And if she’s unhappy, wouldn’t you comfort her? Our mother would have done it—if she were here.”

She wrapped her arms around him. “Our mother is an angel with God,” Arthur sobbed. “And you are the dearest, best woman—pure heart. Teach me my duty. Pray for me. God bless you—God bless you, my sister!”

“Amen,” Warrington groaned, head in hands. “She’s right. She can’t do any wrong. That girl… she looks like an angel.”

And indeed, she did. He would remember that smile for years—her radiant face, her curls falling back, blushing and laughing, still gazing fondly at Arthur. For a moment, she rested her hand on the table, tapping it gently. “And now,” she said, turning to the men, “what shall we do?”

George blinked. “What now?”

“We’ll have some tea,” said Miss Laura, smiling.

But before the unromantic conclusion could unfold, a servant arrived with news: Major Pendennis had returned to the hotel and was waiting to see his nephew.

Laura exchanged a glance with Arthur—one that said, *Behave. Be gentle. Be firm. Do your duty.* Then she retreated to her room.

Warrington, who usually avoided tea like the plague, now wished it were still ten minutes away. Why couldn’t the old man have come later? Or sooner? It didn’t matter. The hour struck. The moment came. Farewell. Hand shaken. Door closed. Friend gone. Joy over. You are alone.

“In which window does her light shine?” he wondered, walking down the street. He strode into the smoking-room of a nearby club, lit a cigar, and joined the chaos—men shouting about politics, opera girls, horse races, and the tyranny of the committee. He sat among them, bearing the sacred secret, listening to their jokes and wild stories. How strange, he thought, to stand in the smoke and noise, knowing every man here carries a secret of his own—sitting quietly apart, far from the game.

Arthur, meanwhile, marched through the hotel corridors, anger rising like a tide. He couldn’t believe his uncle had used him as a puppet, compromising his honour, dragging him into this mess.

When he reached the room, the Major was coughing, grumbling about the fire, complaining that Frosch couldn’t bring his dressing gown or arrange his papers properly—unlike that “d——d impudent scoundrel” Morgan.

“The confounded rascal!” the old man muttered. “Drunk last night, challenged me to a duel—begad, I nearly stabbed him. And he’s made ten thousand pounds! Deserves to be hanged. But curse him, I wish he’d lasted out my time. He knew my ways. When I rang the bell, he brought exactly what I wanted. Unlike that stupid German lout.”

Arthur clenched his jaw. “How have you been, Uncle?”

“Been a good deal with Lady Rockminster?” the Major asked. “Can’t do better. She’s of the old school—vieille ecole, bonne ecole, hey? Dammy, they don’t make gentlemen and ladies anymore. In fifty years, you won’t know one man from another. But they’ll last my time. I ain’t long for this world. Getting very old, Pen. My boy. And I was thinking today, packing up my little library—there’s a Bible among the books. Belonged to my poor mother. I’d like you to keep it.”

Arthur felt a pang. The old man’s kindness softened his anger. He knew what he was about to say would shatter the Major’s dreams.

“I went to your lodgings at Bury Street,” Arthur began slowly. “I spoke with Morgan.”

“Indeed?” The Major’s face flushed. “The cat’s out of the bag now, begad!”

“He told me something that shocked and pained me deeply,” Arthur said.

The Major tried to seem casual. “Ah—about—about—what-d’-you-call-’em, hey?”

“About Miss Amory’s father. About Lady Clavering’s first husband. Who he is. What he is.”

“Hem—a dev’lish awkward affair,” the old man said, rubbing his nose. “I—I’ve known about it for some time.”

Arthur sighed. “I wish I had known earlier—or not at all.”

“You’re right,” the Major said, relieved. “There’s no reason for those two poor women to know. I’d never tell them—though that villain Morgan might.”

“He wants to trade on his secret,” Arthur said grimly. “Already asked me for money to stay silent. I wish I’d known this sooner. It’s not pleasant to think I’m engaged to a convict’s daughter.”

“Exactly why I kept it from you, my dear boy,” the Major said. “But Miss Amory isn’t a convict’s daughter. She’s Lady Clavering’s daughter—with fifty or sixty thousand pounds. Her father-in-law, a Baronet, approves the match and gives up his seat. What could be simpler?”

“Is that true?” Arthur asked.

“Begad, yes. Of course it’s true. Amory’s dead. I tell you he’s dead. The first sign of life he shows, he’s dead. We’ve got him at a deadlock, like the fellow in *The Critic*—dev’lish amusing play. Monstrous witty man, Sheridan. By Gad, when I was at the Cape…”

The old man rambled on, clearly trying to avoid the real issue. But Arthur interrupted.

“If you’d told me this sooner,” he said, “you’d have spared us both pain. I’d never have entered into this engagement—knowing what I now know.”

“No, begad, we’ve fixed you,” the Major said. “A man with a seat in Parliament and a pretty girl with two thousand a year? That’s no bad thing.”

“Great heavens, sir!” Arthur cried. “Are you blind? Can’t you see?”

“See what?”

“That rather than trade on this secret,” Arthur said, “I’d join my father-in-law at the hulks! That rather than take a seat in Parliament as a bribe from Clavering, I’d throw the spoons off the table! You’ve given me a felon’s daughter for a wife—doomed me to poverty and shame! You’ve cursed my career! We’ve been playing a guilty game, and we’ve been overreached. I’ve been degrading myself—selling my soul for money and a seat. And now I’ve lost both.”

“What in Heaven’s name do you mean?” the old man shrieked.

“I mean,” Arthur said, “that there’s a line of baseness I will not cross. I’ve sinned—but I will sin no more. I will let Clavering off that bargain. I’ll take no more than Blanche’s original fortune. I’ll try to make her happy. You brought this on me, sir. But you didn’t know better. I forgive you.”

“Arthur—in God’s name—in your father’s name, who was the proudest man alive—don’t fling this chance away! I beg you! On my knees, I implore you! This is your making! You’ll be a Baronet! Three thousand a year! Don’t do this!”

And with that, the old man actually sank to his knees, clutching Arthur’s hand, tears streaming down his wrinkled face.

“Ah, sir,” Arthur said, groaning. “You’ve caused me enough pain. Spare me this. I’ll marry her. For God’s sake, rise!”

“You mean to take her as a beggar?” the Major gasped, rising and coughing violently.

“I see her as a person who’s suffered a great calamity. And I promised her. She can’t help her misfortune. I won’t withdraw my word now. I won’t take Clavering’s seat unless he offers it freely. I won’t take a penny more than her original fortune.”

“Ring the bell,” the old man said, voice breaking. “I’ve said my say. I’m a dev’lish old fellow. And—it doesn’t matter. Shakespeare was right. Cardinal Wolsey—‘And had I but served my God as I’ve served you.’ Yes. On my knees, by Jove, to my own nephew—I mightn’t have been—Good night, sir. You needn’t call again.”

Arthur took his hand. It was cold and limp. The Major looked older, broken. The next day, he stayed in bed, refusing to see anyone.

And so ended the chapter where the truth came out—and the dream died.

CHAPTER LXXII. In which the Decks Begin to Clear (With a Side of Sarcasm and a Dash of Mourning)

When Arthur Pendennis, resplendent in his dressing-gown like a man who’d just wrestled with the morning and won, made his way up to Warrington’s chambers—his usual post-interview ritual—he found not George, nor even a ghostly echo of him, but Mrs. Flanagan, the laundress, presiding over the ruins of a once-happy literary life.

George was gone. Vanished into the Suffolk countryside like a shy fox fleeing the spotlight. His carpet-bag was packed, his address scribbled on an envelope: “To my brother’s house.” The table was littered with half-finished manuscripts, inkstands oozing like sad tears, pens abandoned mid-sentence, and a blotting-paper that looked like it had seen more drama than a Victorian melodrama. One candle had burned out. The other? Still burning, but only because someone forgot to blow it out—probably George, too busy writing about human folly to notice his own.

“Found him at the desk, sir,” Mrs. Flanagan announced, as if she were reporting on a murder scene. “Writing away like a madman. Hard bed, no sleep. Not a wink all night.”

Ah, yes. The noble life of the writer: where genius is born in exhaustion, and inspiration comes only after 3 a.m., when the world has given up on you and your liver has already surrendered.

Pen stared around, taking in the wreckage—the open drawers, the torn-up pages, the sealing wax chewed into something resembling abstract art. And then he saw it: a gap in the bookcase. Right next to the old College Plato with the Boniface Arms. Helen’s Bible was gone.

Of course it was. George had taken it. Pen didn’t need to read between the lines. He knew why George had fled—not just from family pressure, but from love itself. Love, like a poorly timed sneeze, had caught him off guard. And now, dear, dear old George was off to Norfolk, or wherever the winds blew him, leaving behind only silence and a few scattered thoughts.

Pen sank into George’s chair, feeling suddenly very small. He missed him. He missed his voice, his laughter, his unwavering decency. How could anyone be so good? So honest? So *unbearably* admirable?

“I suppose I’m just a selfish little worm,” Pen mused aloud, though no one was there to hear. “He’s a knight. I’m a tourist in a castle.”

Then, descending to his own chambers, he noticed a note in the letter-box. From George. Written in that familiar, slightly sloping hand:

> “Dear Pen,—
> I shall be half-way home when you breakfast, and intend to stay over Christmas, in Norfolk, or elsewhere.
> I have my own opinion of the issue of matters about which we talked in J——— St. yesterday; and think my presence _de trop_.
> Vale. G. W.
> Give my very best regards and adieux to your cousin.”

And thus, George vanished. The laundress remained. The world moved on.

Pen, dutiful son that he was, went to see his uncle the next day. Not being admitted, he turned to Lady Rockminster’s apartments—where the old lady immediately demanded Bluebeard.

“Bluebeard is gone,” Pen said, pulling out the note.

Laura took it, glanced at it, handed it back without looking at him, and walked away.

Pen, undeterred, launched into a full-blown eulogy of George—so passionate, so heartfelt, that Lady Rockminster blinked in surprise. She’d never heard him praise anyone this much. “You’ve always been cold, Arthur,” she said. “I didn’t know you cared so deeply.”

“Well,” Pen replied, “he’s not just a friend. He’s a man who’d give you his last crust—and then ask if you wanted the crumbs.”

---

Later, walking through Waterloo Place (because what else does one do when one is emotionally drained and duty-bound?), Pen spotted a familiar figure emerging from Messrs. Gimcrack’s famous shop. It was Foker—Harry Foker, heir to the great brewery empire, and now, by the looks of it, the new king of the ale trade.

He was in deep mourning—black hat, black gloves, black brougham, black horse, black driver. Even the springs seemed mournful. A grieving man, clearly, though one might question whether grief or greed was the real emotion beneath the velvet.

“Hail, Foker!” Pen called out, catching up with him. “What brings you to the land of jewels and regret?”

Foker’s tiny hand—gloved in deepest ebony, wristband snow-white—extended toward Arthur. The other hand clutched a morocco case, likely containing a watch, a ring, or perhaps a lock of hair from a recently deceased fiancée.

Pen’s mind raced. This wasn’t just mourning. This was *accessory*. He saw the pattern: the heir, fresh from his father’s funeral, indulging in trinkets while the world watched. Like Horace’s young prince pouring wine from his father’s casks—only here, the wine was diamonds and gold.

“Le Roi est mort. Vive le Roi!” Pen declared.

“Ah,” said Foker, blushing like a schoolgirl caught stealing cake. “Yes. Thank you. Very much obliged. How do you do, Pen?—very busy—good-bye!”

And off he went, jumping into his black carriage like a tiny, anxious monarch, sipping tea and trying not to look guilty.

Pen chuckled. “Ah, the wheel turns. Old Harry’s dead. New Harry’s drunk on inheritance. The bankers bow. The draymen cheer. And somewhere, a poor soul wonders if they’ll ever get their twenty thousand a yard.”

---

Later, speaking with Laura, Pen mused aloud on the nature of money and loyalty.

“You know,” he said, “there was no thought of money between me and our dear mother. Laura.”

“Of course not,” she said, eyes brightening. “You would have spurned it!”

“Oh, come now,” Pen said, feigning seriousness. “Don’t pretend I’m some angelic martyr. I might have felt the pull. But thank God, I didn’t have to choose. I’m glad I didn’t face the temptation.”

“That’s not true,” Laura insisted, her voice soft. “You wouldn’t have sold yourself. You couldn’t.”

“And yet,” Pen said, “we’re all taught to pray for deliverance from evil. I’m glad I was spared that sin. But I wonder—what if I had been tempted? What if I had known the taste of betrayal?”

Laura looked at him, heartbroken. “Arthur, don’t say such things. You frighten me.”

“Do I?” he asked gently. “Then let me tell you what But said to me just now.”

“But?” she whispered.

“Yes. But. That sneaky little word. It creeps in when you least expect it. ‘If that girl had reason as well as love,’ But said, ‘she’d love you no more.’ If she knew you as you are—the flawed, selfish creature you know—you’d lose her. You’d lose everything.”

Laura shivered.

“And yet,” Pen added, “you’re pure. You’re untouched. You’re like a child who doesn’t know what a lie is. I think some of you—some women—were never fallen. You bear sweet thoughts like flowers. You can’t help it.”

“Then what am I?” Laura asked.

“A miracle,” he said. “And I’m afraid I’m the skeptic who keeps trying to dissect it.”

---

Lady Rockminster entered, having completed her daily ritual of transformation—like a phoenix rising from the ashes of bathwater and perfume.

“Mr. Pendennis,” she said, “you’re always coming here.”

“It’s pleasant,” Arthur said. “We were just talking about Foker.”

“He’s a fine young man,” she said. “Fifteen thousand a year. My cousin. He must come and see me.”

She paused, then added, “He’s engaged to Lady Ann.”

“Engaged?” Arthur asked.

“Engaged? No,” she said, frowning. “She broke it off. Married someone else. Mr. Hobson. Her father’s chaplain. Red-haired widower. Two children. Poor Lord Rosherville is distraught.”

“Married?” Arthur repeated. “In Drummington Park?”

“Yes. Last Thursday. After breakfast. With her maid. In the church. No notice. No ceremony. Just—gone.”

Pen blinked. “So Foker’s free?”

“Free?” Lady Rockminster said. “He’s his own master now. And he’ll choose for himself. Probably Alice or Barbara. Alice has smallpox. Barbara is ten years older. Not promising.”

Pen smiled. “Well, that’s one less problem for me.”

But his mind wandered. Who was Foker buying those bracelets for? Was it Lady Ann? Or someone else? Someone younger? Someone French? Someone who wore feathers?

The world was full of mysteries. And none of them mattered quite as much as the one unfolding in Tunbridge Wells.

Because Laura, bless her, was determined to marry Blanche *now*. She wrote letters. She implored. She threatened to run away. She even offered to go to Tunbridge—despite Lady Rockminster’s protests—just to be near her new sister.

“Must go,” Laura wrote. “Will go. Will not be deterred.”

And so began the correspondence:

**Blanche, No. 1:** “Oh, dearest Laura! I shall welcome you with open arms! We’ll sing duets! Wander through golden woods! I count the hours!”

**Laura, No. 2:** “How lovely! I hope our friendship grows stronger each day. We’ll share secrets. We’ll make each other happy.”

**Blanche, No. 3:** “Wait. What? Your letter says nothing about Arthur. What’s happened? What is this mystery?”

And thus, the mystery deepened. Until Arthur, finally, could no longer resist. He picked up his pen. He folded the paper. And wrote the letter that would change everything.

But that, dear reader, is a story for another chapter.

Dear Blanche,

You’re always writing novels in your head—dramas with grand entrances, tragic misunderstandings, and lovers who meet under moonlit balconies. Well, here’s one for you: not the fairy-tale ending where the heroine inherits a castle and marries a prince, but the far more realistic version—where the heroine discovers she’s been tricked into marrying a man whose only fortune is a broken teacup and a questionable future.

Let’s be honest: I’m not offering you the life of a duchess. I’m offering you the life of a man who’s just realized that all his dreams have gone up in smoke like a poorly lit cigar. No stars on my coat. No title. No mansion in Belgravia (though I did once dream of it). Instead, I offer you me—a writer with two half-finished novels and a flair for melodrama. I’ll be called to the Bar, maybe even get a colonial post if I work harder than I ever have in my life (which, let’s face it, is asking a lot). And I’ll buy back the *Pall Mall Gazette*—it’s practically free now that poor Shandon died, and the publishers are eager to offload it. Warrington will help me run it. He’ll write the editorials, I’ll write the satire, and together we’ll make it respectable enough to sell copies. You can supervise the fashion column, break hearts in Poet’s Corner, and occasionally pretend you’re part of the elite. We’ll live over the offices in Catherine Street, Strand—four rooms, a kitchen, and a garret for Laura. Or perhaps we’ll move to Waterloo Road? It’s lovely, really—except for that pesky halfpenny toll at the bridge.

The boys can go to King’s College, yes? That’s settled. Does this sound like a joke?

No. It’s not a joke. It’s the truth. The carriage has vanished. The house in Belgravia? Gone with the wind. My seat in Parliament? Cancelled. I’ve written to Sir Francis Clavering, explaining I cannot accept the nomination until after marriage. And I won’t take any larger fortune from you than what you’ve always had—since your grandfather died and your half-brother was born. Your mother doesn’t know why I’ve made this decision. She shouldn’t. The reason is painful, unavoidable, and as final as Alnaschar kicking over his porcelain. Once shattered, no amount of glue or goodwill can mend it.

So here’s the question: Will you marry me anyway? Not because I’m rich or noble, but because you love me? Because you’re willing to walk beside me through humdrum days, mending my socks, sharing a cab when we’re lucky, and laughing at the absurdity of it all? If you say yes, come to me, Blanche. God help me, I’ll do my best to make you happy. If not—if you’d rather climb the social ladder and ride in a chariot while I stand in the crowd watching—I’ll understand. I’ll even smile at you from the sidewalk. I saw Lady Mirabel last season—her husband glittering with medals, her coachman wearing flowers like a peacock. Would you prefer that? Or would you rather walk beside me, hand in hand, through ordinary life?

I can’t tell you yet what happened—what changed everything. But it’s done. I’ve given up my ambitions. I’ve accepted my fate. And if you’ll have me, I’ll accept yours.

Now, about other news: Uncle Pendennis is unwell and furious that I turned down the seat. He’d planned it all. Poor old fellow. But Warrington, Laura, and I have stood by me. They know the secret. They support me. George is noble. Laura is our saint. With such friends, who needs the world?

---

Blanche’s reply arrived—not to me, but to Laura first, then to me. It was written in her neatest hand, on her scented paper, full of poetry and pain. But the meaning? That was a puzzle.

She wrote:
“You are spoiled by the world. You don’t love me as I deserve. You offer me lightly—take me or leave me. But I want more than that. I want your whole soul. I want to be your everything. You speak of separation with a smile. You don’t hurry to see me. Is life nothing but disillusion? I’ve wept. I’ve prayed. I’ve shed bitter tears over your letter. You treat my love like dross—like something cheap. You ask me to bow to you, as if I were a servant. Is this how love ends? For me? Always?”

And then she signed: “B.”

I read it twice. Three times. I laughed—sardonically. What a mess! What a beautiful, heartbreaking mess! She loves me—but thinks I don’t love her. She wants to stay—but fears I’ll abandon her. She’s torn between duty and desire. And I’m left wondering: does she accept me? Or reject me? Is she surrendering to me out of pity—or is she rejecting me because she knows too much?

I sat there, twirling the letter like a magician’s wand, enjoying the irony of it all. Then my servant came in with a card: “Mr. Samuel Huxter, on particular business.”

I invited him in—amused. And there he stood, sucking his stick, eyes wide, face pale, looking like a man who’d just seen a ghost.

“Pray sit,” I said, “and tell me what trouble has found you.”

“I’m in a fix,” he said, grimly.

“Indeed?”

“She sent me to you,” he continued.

“Fanny?” I asked. “Is she well? I meant to visit her.”

“I heard through my governor and Jack Hobnell,” Huxter said. “Congratulations, sir—on the borough and the lady. Fanny sends her congratulations too.” He blushed.

“There’s many a slip between the cup and the lip,” I said. “Who knows what may happen?”

“I wish you could speak to my father,” H.
“Tell him what?”

“I’ve done it,” he said, dropping his voice. “I’ve married her.”

I jumped up. “You—you didn’t—do anything wrong to that dear creature?”

“I hope not,” he said, hanging his head. “But I’ve married her. And now my father’s coming to town. He’ll be here tomorrow. Then it all comes out.”

“And when did this happen?” I asked.

“Five weeks ago. Two days after Miss Amory came to Shepherd’s Inn.”

Ah. So that’s why Blanche was so quiet lately. She knew. She’d known for a month. And she wasn’t going to implicate her father. She was letting me off the hook—gently, gracefully, with a letter full of sorrow and sacrifice.

“Do you know who Altamont is?” Huxter asked suddenly.

We both looked at each other. I remembered the scene at Chambers—the hysterical woman, the crying girl, the fuming colonel. “Altamont,” I said slowly, “is Mrs. Lightfoot’s first husband. Come back to life.”

Huxter nodded. “Fanny and I think so. Maybe Lightfoot won’t mind.”

Jealousy flared in his eyes. Poor fool. Now that he was married, he still thought Fanny loved me.

“Let’s talk about your affairs,” I said. “How can I help?”

“He thinks you can,” Huxter said, taking my hand. “He says you might talk to my father. Break the news gently. My mother—she’s a clergyman’s daughter. She hates being lower than us. Fanny’s not from a good family. But she’s a Huxter now.”

“The wife takes the husband’s rank,” I said.

“And with practice,” Huxter added, “she’ll be better than any girl in Clavering. You should hear her sing. Old Bows taught her. She’s got talent. Might even go on stage if things fall apart. But I’d rather not.”

“She’s a coquette,” I said.

“She can’t help it,” Huxter said. “If there are twenty men in a room, and one ignores her, she won’t rest until the twentieth is at her side.”

“Then bring her mother,” I said, laughing.

“She must keep the lodge. I can’t stay with that lot. Consider my rank.”

“Au fait,” I said, amused. “And mutato nomine, this story could be told of anyone—anywhere.”

Just then, another knock. The servant announced: “Mr. Bows.”

In walked the old music teacher—pale, trembling, face flushed. He sat down, hands on knees, sun shining on his bald head. I looked at him with sympathy. This man had loved Fanny. He’d given her lessons, time, affection. And she’d chosen someone else.

“So we’ve lost,” I said. “Here is the lucky winner.”

“Yes, sir,” Bows said quietly. “Here is the lucky winner.”

“I suppose you came from my place?” Huxter asked.

“Yes,” Bows said. “Mrs. Sam sent me. She thought you might do something stupid—something like yourself.”

“Big fools,” growled Huxter.

“A few,” Bows said. “Not many, I hope.”

“She wanted me to deliver a message,” Bows continued. “To you. But you wouldn’t give it. So she sent me.”

Huxter blushed crimson.

Pen laughed. The scene delighted me—this mix of jealousy, pride, and absurdity.

“I’ll pay my respects to your wife,” I said.

“In Charterhouse Lane,” Bows said. “Over the baker’s. St. John’s Street leads into Smithfield. Doctor Johnson used to walk there with ragged shoes and a bundle of penny-a-lining.”

“I’ve seen worse luck than mine,” I said. “I’ve seen quacks succeed and brave men fail. Don’t think I’m proud because I’ve won. The prizes of life aren’t for the deserving. You’re as good as I am. It’s just that fortune favors some, and not others.”

Bows wiped his forehead. “Perhaps you’re right. I’ve been grumbling. Most men do when they think about themselves.”

“I don’t know what you mean,” Huxter said, confused.

“Mrs. H. sent me to watch over you,” Bows said. “She knew you wouldn’t deliver the message. And she knew I would. She wanted you to come back.”

Huxter stood up, grabbed his hat, and fled.

“I thought that would send him out,” Bows said, sinking back into his chair.

“It’s all true,” he continued. “She wants you back. She cajoles everyone—me, Costigan, the young doctors from Bartholomew’s. She’s got a little court already. If there’s no one around, she practices on the baker or the black sweeper.”

“Is she fond of him?” I asked.

“There’s no accounting for likes,” Bows said. “Yes, she is. She decided to marry him. They published banns at St. Clement’s. Nobody knew. One day she slipped out of the lodge, married him, and went to Gravesend. Left a note for me to explain to her mother. Bless you, the old woman knew all along.”

“I miss her,” Bows said. “Tripping down the court. Coming for her lesson. Now the lodge feels empty. I go and sit outside her door like an old fool. But she keeps it neat. She sings like a lark. She cooks his dinner. She’s happy. What’s the use of being angry? I lent them three pounds. They’ve got nothing till the reconciliation.”

When Bows left, I took Blanche’s letter and Huxter’s news to Laura.

“Doesn’t it seem odd?” I asked. “That she avoids asking why I’ve changed my mind?”

Laura was pale. “It seems to me,” she said, “that Blanche is playing a game. She wants to keep her options open.”

“And if there’s someone else?” I asked.

“You’re not free, Arthur,” Laura said calmly. “You belong to another. And I can’t help thinking… she knows. She knows her father’s secret.”

And so we sat, two cousins, two people caught in the web of fate, love, and misfortune—while the world kept turning, and the next chapter waited to unfold.

CHAPTER LXXIV. How Arthur Should Have Bought a Return Ticket (And Other Regrets)

The train, that merciless express of modern life, deposited Arthur Pendennis at Tunbridge with all the efficiency of a well-oiled machine—too efficiently, in fact. He had barely time to reflect on his entire existence before arriving at his destination, which was already, in his mind, the end of the line. As he rattled along through the countryside, he reviewed his past with the solemnity of a man who’d just been handed a bill for a lifetime of bad decisions.

“Here it ends,” he mused, staring out the window as if expecting to see a signpost reading *Regret Ahead*. “Romance? Ambition? Dreams? All gone. I refuse an angel—my mother begs, and I say no! And yet, if I had taken her, would she have been an angel? Probably not. She’d have been a wife forced upon me by my uncle’s matchmaking, like some prepackaged emotional package. I couldn’t have loved her honestly. I needed to discover her myself, not through someone else’s eyes. But now look—here I am, accepting Blanche, a seat in Parliament, riches, and a career—all guaranteed by Uncle Pendennis, and what do I get? Fortune comes, drops off the dowry, and leaves me holding only the woman. The heart without the money. What a bargain!”

He sighed, then turned to the priest sitting across from him, who hadn’t blinked since Reigate Tunnel. “I wish I were him,” Arthur thought. “At least he has a breviary. A clear path. No doubts. No guilt. Just pages of prayer and a quiet soul.” Then he glanced at the old gentleman next to the priest, scowling at him over a newspaper like a man who’d just discovered the world was built on lies. “And I wish I were him too,” Arthur added, “at least he has something to hate.”

Just then, the stout gentleman beside him leaned forward. “Would you care to read the paper, sir?” he asked, offering a copy with a headline screaming about the evils of black-coated men (a group clearly responsible for all national sins).

Arthur took it, nodded politely, and then promptly forgot he was holding it. His mind wandered back to deeper matters: faith, truth, destiny. “Who could understand another’s inner chaos?” he pondered. “Who can measure the weight of a childhood fear, a missed chance, a single misstep? A grain of sand might shift the course of a river—or a man’s life. Who but God knows the full account?”

He bowed his head, not in prayer exactly, but in surrender. “If I marry Blanche,” he whispered to himself, “may I be true. May I make her happy. If I have children, may I teach them honesty. May they inherit a name that isn’t stained by my cowardice or my vanity.”

Then the train stopped. Tunbridge. His moment had arrived.

He handed the newspaper back to the stout gentleman and stepped out, carpet-bag in hand, determined to face whatever awaited him. A fly whisked him to Lady Clavering’s house, where he composed a little speech—a noble, sincere, almost poetic declaration of love and honor, though slightly undermined by the fact that he was delivering it to a woman he no longer wanted to marry.

The gist:
“Blanche, I don’t know what your last letter meant. Is my proposal acceptable? I’ve given up the wealth and status because I couldn’t accept them without feeling dishonored. If you doubt my affection, let’s marry right now. I’ll keep my vow. I’ll cherish you. I’ll be a loving husband.”

He sprang from the fly, marched to the door, and was met by a servant who looked utterly baffled.
“Her Ladyship’s not at home, sir,” the man said.
“I’m Mr. Pendennis,” Arthur replied.
“Lightfoot is gone,” the man continued. “My Lady is out, and my orders were——”
“I hear Miss Amory singing,” Arthur interrupted. “Take my bag to a dressing room.”

He strode into the drawing room, where Blanche sat at the piano, belting out a melodramatic tune so passionate it made the air crackle. Master Clavering slept on the sofa, blissfully unaware. But near Blanche sat a gentleman—Foker—who looked like he’d just won the lottery, a gold medal, and the keys to heaven.

As Arthur entered, Foker jumped. The music cut off mid-note. Blanche shrieked. Frank Clavering woke up, yawned, and muttered, “Here’s a jolly go!” before vanishing in a snigger.

Arthur froze. Then he saw it.

On the piano, open and glowing like a jewel thief’s dream, was a purple-leather box. Inside, curled around white satin, lay a serpentine bracelet—diamond tail, ruby head—so magnificent it seemed to hum with power. The same kind Harry had shown him three days earlier in Waterloo Place.

“Hullo!” Foker said, trying to sound casual.
“How de-do, Pendennis?”
Blanche trembled, fumbled with her handkerchief, and tried to cover the bracelet. Then she stepped forward, hands shaking, and greeted Arthur.
“How is dearest Laura?” she asked.

Foker, meanwhile, looked like a man caught red-handed in a crime he didn’t know was illegal. His ears turned crimson. His cheeks flushed. Arthur stared. Then—*BAM!*—he erupted into laughter. Not polite chuckles. Not a smirk. A full-blown, thunderous, “Oh dear heavens, this is absurd!” fit that startled Blanche more than any funeral procession.

“And this,” Arthur gasped between laughs, “was the secret? You’re a marvel, Foker! A model of loyalty! Could I possibly stand between Blanche and such unwavering devotion? Could I stand between Miss Amory and fifteen thousand pounds a year?”

“It is not that!” Blanche declared, puffing up with dignity. “It is not money. It is not rank. It is constancy. It is fidelity. It is a whole, trusting, loving heart offered to me—that I treasure!”

She reached for her handkerchief—but paused. Then slowly, deliberately, she put it down. “I do not disown,” she said, “that I once thought I loved you. I believed I was beloved. I clung to that hope. I prayed. I longed. But your words—your coldness—your indifference—undid me. You trifled with my heart. You flung back my troth with scorn. I’ve told Mr. Foker everything.”

“That you have,” Foker said, eyes shining with devotion.
