“Everything?” Arthur asked, raising an eyebrow.
“It is I who am in fault,” Blanche said. “I won’t appeal. I bear it in silence.”
Arthur smiled sadly. “I came here expecting one thing. Heaven knows what I expected. But not this. I hope you’ll be happy with him. Truly. He deserves you.”

Foker, overwhelmed, burst into tears. “She don’t know—she can’t know how much I love her. I’m just a poor little beggar. And she takes me—says she’ll try—and I—I—love her. I ain’t worthy. Give us your hand, old boy. She forgives you. Let’s be friends.”

They shook hands. Arthur, half-laughing, half-sighing, felt oddly relieved.
“Only one person here needs pity,” he said. “You, Blanche.”

“You’re an angel!” Foker cried, wiping his eyes.
Blanche looked up, radiant, like a saint in a Victorian painting. “In spite of everything,” she said, “I must always regard Arthur as a brother. We’ve known each other years. We’ve walked the same paths. Plucked the same flowers. Arthur! Henry! Take hands and be friends!”

They did. Foker kissed Blanche’s hand, weeping. She placed her other hand gently on his head. “Foolish boy,” she murmured. “It shall be loved as it deserves. Who could resist such a silly creature?”

Frank Clavering returned, bursting in. “Pendennis! The man wants to be paid and go back. He’s had some beer.”
“I’ll go back with him,” Arthur said. “Good-bye, Blanche. God bless you, Foker. You two don’t need me here.”

But Blanche stopped him. “Wait. One word. In private.”

Foker, thrilled by the mention of “Henry,” practically danced away. “Trust you? Oh, who wouldn’t trust you?”

Alone, Blanche spoke calmly. “You never loved me, Mr. Pendennis.”
“I told you how much,” Arthur said. “I never deceived you.”
“You’re going to Laura tonight, aren’t you?”
“Was that what you had to say?”
“You never cared for me.”
“Et vous?”
“Et moi, c’est différent. I’ve been spoiled early. I cannot live without excitement. Without emotion. You offered neither. You’re bored with everything—even ambition. You had a career. You gave it up—for what? For a betise? For a scruple? Why not take the seat? Be a puritan? Why refuse what is mine by right?”

Arthur blinked. “You know all this?”
“Only within a month. But I suspected since Baymouth. N’importe. It’s not too late. He’s as if he never existed. There’s still a place in the world for you. Why not sit in Parliament? Use your talent? Give yourself a future? I take celui-là. Il est bon. Il est riche. Il est—vous le connaissez autant que moi enfin.”

She paused. “Think you that I would not prefer un homme qui fera parler de moi? If the secret comes out, I’m rich beyond millions. How does it affect me? It’s not my fault. It will never come out.”

Arthur stared. “You’ll tell Harry everything?”
“Je comprends. Vous refusez.” She said it with a sneer. “I’ll tell Harry when we’re married. You won’t betray me, will you? You, who hold a defenseless girl’s secret—will you use it against her? S’il me plait de le cacher, mon secret; pourquoi le donnerai-je? Je l’aime, mon pauvre père, voyez-vous? I’d rather live with that man than with you, your fades intriguers of the world. I must have emotions—il m’en donne. Il m’écrit. Il écrit très bien—comme un pirate—comme un Bohémien—comme un homme.”

Arthur sighed. “The pirate would tire you, like the rest.”
“Eh! Il me faut des émotions,” she insisted.

And there it was—the truth of Blanche Amory. Arthur had never seen her so clearly, nor so completely. But he also saw more than she was. Because beneath the passion, the drama, the fire—there was only performance. A sham enthusiasm. A fake grief. A counterfeit love. Each emotion blazed for a moment, then fizzled out, replaced by the next theatrical spark.

And as Arthur walked away, carrying his carpet-bag and his broken dreams, he realized something:
He should have bought a return ticket.

CHAPTER LXXV. A Chapter of Match-Making (With a Side of Drama and a Dash of Deception)

On the Tunbridge platform, Pen was practically vibrating with impatience—like a kettle left on too high a flame—until the evening train to London finally arrived. Half an hour passed, but to him it felt like six years, or possibly the entire duration of human history. The train came, it sped off into the twilight, and just as the lights of London blinked into view like a city’s sleepy eyes, a gentleman—forgetting his carpet bag in the process—dashed out, hopped into a cab, and bellowed, “Drive as fast as you can to Jermyn Street!”

The cabman, despite being a hansom-cabman (which, let’s be honest, is no small thing), accepted the tip with a polite “Thank you” and drove off at a speed that made the horses look like they were auditioning for *The Flying Dutchman*. Meanwhile, Pen bounded up the stairs of the hotel, heart hammering like a drum solo, straight to Lady Rockminster’s apartments.

Inside, Laura sat alone in the drawing-room, reading by lamplight, her face pale as a winter moon. She looked up when he entered. Should we follow? Well, why not? Life’s great moments are usually just ordinary ones dressed up in dramatic lighting and emotional music. Sometimes all it takes is one word, one glance, or a hand squeeze so intense it could power a small nation.

As Lady Rockminster stirred from her post-dinner nap and sauntered into the sitting room, we might as well tag along. “Upon my word, young people!” she declared, her voice sharp as a pair of scissors, while her attendant peered over her shoulder with eyes wide enough to swallow a walnut. And rightly so. Because there, in the middle of the room, stood Pen—on his knees—holding Laura’s trembling hand like a man who’d just discovered the secret of eternal life.

It had all happened in a flash: Pen burst in, saw Laura, didn’t even let her say “Back so soon?” before snatching her hand and collapsing to the floor like a fallen oak tree. “I’ve seen her,” he gasped. “She’s engaged to Harry Foker—and—and now, Laura?”

Laura’s hand squeezed back. Her eyes sparkled. Her lips trembled—but said nothing. And then, like a scene from a particularly melodramatic play, Pen buried his head in her lap and sobbed, “Come and bless us, dear mother.”

And then—cue the tender arms of Helen, wrapped around him like a warm blanket of forgiveness.

Just as things were getting emotionally charged, Lady Rockminster marched in and boomed, “Upon my word, young people! Beck! Leave the room. What do you think you’re doing, poking your nose in here?”

Pen leapt up, still clutching Laura’s hand like a lifeline. “She’s consoling me for my misfortune, ma’am,” he announced proudly. “What do you mean by kissing her hand? I don’t know what you’ll do next.”

“Oh,” said the old lady, narrowing her eyes. “You kissed *my* hand?”

“Yes, ma'am,” Pen said, unrepentant. “I’ve been to Tunbridge. I saw Miss Amory. And I found—well, a villain has stolen my place in her affections.”

“Is that all?” Lady Rockminster snapped. “Is that what you were crying about on your knees? You might have waited till tomorrow.”

“But another has taken my place,” Pen continued, adopting a tragically noble tone. “But why call him a villain? He’s brave, constant, young, wealthy, and beautiful.”

“What nonsense are you spouting?” the old lady demanded. “What’s really happened?”

“Miss Amory has jilted me,” Pen confessed, “and accepted Henry Foker, Esq. I found her singing love songs to him while he lay at her feet. Presents exchanged. Vows sworn. For ten days. Harry was old Mrs. Planter’s rheumatism, which kept dear Laura out of the house. He’s the most generous man alive—he promised Lady Ann’s husband the living of Logwood and gave her a magnificent wedding gift. And as soon as he was free, he rushed to throw himself at Blanche’s feet.”

“And so,” Lady Rockminster said, folding her arms, “since you can’t have Blanche, you settle for Laura? Is that it?”

“No, ma'am,” Pen replied. “He acted nobly. I did as she asked.”

“You may have done,” the old lady muttered. “But if you think I’m not worthy of Laura, I know it. And I pray Heaven to make me better. And if loving the best and purest creature in the world helps, then I shall have that joy to help me.”

“Hm, hm,” Lady Rockminster grumbled, eyeing them both. “All very well. But I should have preferred Bluebeard.”

At this point, Pen, sensing the conversation was veering toward emotional minefields, thought quickly. He remembered his morning chat with Huxter—about Fanny Bolton’s sudden marriage and the looming arrival of her father. So, to shift the mood, he launched into a story about how Huxter had married Fanny, and how terrified they were of Mr. Huxter senior showing up.

He told it with such comic flair—especially emphasizing Fanny’s flirtatious nature and her irresistible charm—that it was clear he was trying to say: *See, Laura? I wasn’t the one chasing after her. It was she who chased me. And now she’s gone off to charm someone else. Let’s forget that little incident, shall we? Or punish me gently, if you must.*

Laura caught on immediately. “If you did anything wrong,” she said softly, “you repented, dear Pen. And you know…”—she blushed—“that I have no right to reproach you.”

“Hm,” grumbled Lady Rockminster. “Still, I should have preferred Bluebeard.”

Pen smiled. “The past is gone. The future is ours. I’ll do my best to make your future happy, dear Laura.”

His heart swelled—not with pride, but with awe. He loved her more now that she’d admitted feeling something for Warrington. It showed her honesty, her depth. And Laura? She was probably thinking: *How strange that I ever cared for anyone else. I barely feel sorry he’s gone. In fact, I’m almost annoyed I care so little. Oh, Arthur—I’ve learned to love him so much in these two months. My thoughts are all about him. He’s never far from me. And soon… he’ll be mine. Mine! Oh, mother, if only you were here!*

She glowed. Literally. A halo of happiness seemed to surround her. She walked differently, talked differently—like a woman reborn. Arthur noticed. Lady Rockminster noticed. And the old lady whispered, “What a sly little wretch you’ve been, keeping your secret so well.”

Pen, meanwhile, was laughing about Huxter’s wedding and the chaos of it all.

“So,” Laura said, “what are we going to do for the young couple?”

Of course she would care—any true lover loves lovers. “We must go see them,” Pen said.

“Of course,” Laura agreed. “I intend to be very fond of Fanny. Let’s go right now. Lady Rockminster, may I borrow the carriage?”

“Go now?” the old lady scoffed. “It’s eleven o’clock at night. They’re probably in their nightcaps. And it’s time for you to go. Good night, Mr. Pendennis.”

Arthur and Laura begged for ten more minutes.

“Then tomorrow morning,” Lady Rockminster relented. “I’ll come and fetch you with Martha.”

“An earl’s coronet,” Pen mused, “will have quite an effect in Lamb Court and Smithfield. Wait—Lady Rockminster, will you join us in a little conspiracy?”

“A conspiracy?” she raised an eyebrow.

“Will you pretend to be ill tomorrow? When Mr. Huxter senior arrives, I’ll bring him in. If he thinks he’s attending a baronet in the country, what influence won’t a countess have? Once he’s softened, we’ll reveal the truth, bring in the couple, extract the paternal blessing, and finish the comedy.”

“A parcel of nonsense,” Lady Rockminster said. “Now take your hat, sir. Come away, miss. There—my head is turned another way. Good night, young people.”

And perhaps, as she walked away arm-in-arm with Laura, humming softly to herself, she thought of her own youth—of love, loss, and the quiet triumphs of the heart.

Next morning, Laura and Martha arrived as planned. The desired sensation—let’s hope—was achieved in Lamb Court. Then the trio headed to Charterhouse Lane to visit Mr. and Mrs. Samuel Huxter.

Fanny greeted them with a mix of nervousness and pride. She hadn’t seen Pen since her wedding. “Samuel told me how kind you were,” she said. “You were always kind, Mr. Pendennis. And—uh—how is your friend who was ill at Shepherd’s Inn?”

“My name is Laura,” the other woman said, blushing. “I’m Arthur’s sister. We’ll always love you for caring for him when he was sick. When we live in the country, we’ll see each other often. And I’ll always be happy to hear of your happiness, Fanny.”

“We’re going to do what you and Huxter did,” Pen said cheerfully. “Where is Huxter? What lovely lodgings! What a sweet cat!”

As Fanny answered, Laura thought: *Well, now—this is the girl everyone was so worried about? What did he see in her? She’s plain, yes—but what manners! Bless her, she was kind to him.*

Mr. Samuel had gone to meet his father. Mrs. Huxter said the old man would arrive at the Somerset Coffee-house. Fanny was trembling. “If he disowns us, what will we do?” she whispered. “I’ll never forgive myself for bringing shame on my husband’s head. You must intercede for us, Mr. Arthur. If anyone can sway him, it’s you.”

She still saw Pen as a superior being. Arthur, watching her, thought of the past—the little dramas, the tiny fears, the vanity, the sweetness of a new bride.

Afterward, Messrs. Linton and Blades arrived, puffing tobacco smoke like dragons. They’d seen the coronet on the carriage and were awestruck. “Who was that heavy swell who just drove off?” they asked. “And the countess—she’s the right sort.” They’d heard Pen’s father was “only a sawbones” and that he “gave himself confounded airs.”

They also recalled the night Huxter had argued with Pen in the Back Kitchen.

Later, returning through Fleet Street, Laura remarked, “Fanny is very nice, but honestly—no beauty. Maybe there is, but I can’t see it.”

Just then, near Temple Bar, they saw young Huxter returning to his bride. “The governor’s arrived,” he reported. “He’s at the Somerset Coffee-house. He’s in good spirits—something about the railway. But he’s afraid to talk about—about that business. Will Mr. Pendennis try it?”

Pen said he’d go right away.

Huxter junior hid outside while the interview took place. The coronet on the carriage inspired awe in him. And old Mr. Huxter, looking from the coffee-house window, grinned at the Strand—a sight he adored.

“And I can afford to give myself a lark, sir,” he said, shaking hands with Pen. “We got our bill. Our branch line’s approved. Our shares are up. And we’re buying your three fields along the Brawl—putting a pretty penny into your pocket, Mr. Pendennis.”

“Indeed!” Pen said, remembering a letter from Mr. Tatham that he’d neglected to open. “I hope you don’t plan to grow rich and abandon practice.”

“We can’t lose you at Clavering, Mr. Huxter,” Pen added. “Though I hear your son’s doing well. Dr. Goodenough speaks highly of him. It’s hard that a man of your eminence should be stuck in a country town.”

“The metropolis would have been my sphere,” Huxter said, gazing at the Strand. “But a man takes his business where he finds it. I succeeded to my father’s.”

“It was my father’s too,” Pen said. “Sometimes I wish I’d followed it.”

“You’ve chosen a loftier path,” the old man said. “You aspire to the senate. You write poetry. You move in fashionable circles. We keep an eye on you at Clavering. We read your name in aristocratic party lists. Why, only yesterday my wife wondered why your name wasn’t mentioned at the Earl of Kidderminster’s gathering.”

“Whose equipage was that?” Huxter asked, pointing. “The Countess Dowager of Rockminster? How is her Ladyship?”

“She’s not very well,” Pen said. “That’s why I urged her to see you.”

Old Huxter nearly fainted with joy. “There’s an old friend of yours in the carriage—a Clavering lady. Will you come out and speak to her?”

The old surgeon, thrilled, ran out, bowing and grinning.

Huxter junior watched from afar as his father spoke with Laura, saw her extend her hand, and then—after a brief exchange with Pen—saw his father actually jump into the carriage and drive away with Miss Bell.

No room for Arthur. He returned, laughing, and told his father where he’d gone.

During the ride, Laura charmed the old man with flattery, wit, and gentle manipulation. By the time they reached Jermyn Street, he was ready to grant her anything.

Lady Rockminster, meanwhile, played the role of dignified patient, complimenting him on his skill and expressing interest in consulting him. “What are your symptoms?” she asked. “Should I see my usual doctor? Mr. Jones is out of town?”

He would have treated her with the finest remedies—Spir: Ammon: Aromat: with a touch of Spir: Menth: Pip: and orange-flower water. He wrote home raving about her, told Samuel all about her, and even suggested taking her to the theatre.

Next day, at twelve, Mr. Huxter arrived at Jermyn Street. Lady Rockminster was still in bed, but Pen and Laura waited.

She’d had a comfortable night. How had he spent his evening? At the theatre? With his son? “A capital piece,” she said. “Mrs. O’Leary sang beautifully. And young Huxter—such a good fellow. Liked by all. An honour to his profession.”

“He ought to practise in the country,” Arthur said. “He ought to marry—others are doing so. Settle down.”

“Exactly what her Ladyship said yesterday,” Huxter replied. “Sam should marry.”

“The town is full of temptations,” Pen added.

The old man thought of Mrs. O’Leary.

“There’s no better safeguard than early marriage with an honest, affectionate creature.”

“Indeed,” said Miss Bell.

“And love is better than money, isn’t it?”

“Indeed it is,” Miss Bell said.

“I agree with so fair an authority,” the old man bowed.

Then Pen leaned in. “Suppose I had news to tell you.”

“God bless my soul, Mr. Pendennis! What do you mean?”

“Suppose I told you that a young man—carried away by an irresistible passion for an admirable, virtuous young woman—whom everyone falls in love with—consulted reason and heart, and married. Suppose I told you that man is my friend. That the Countess Dowager of Rockminster is deeply interested in him—and you can imagine what a young man can achieve with THAT family behind him. Suppose I told you that you know him—that he’s here—that he is——”

“Sam married! God bless my soul, you don’t mean that!”

“And to such a nice creature, dear Mr. Huxter.”

“Her Ladyship is charmed with her,” Pen said—lying for the first time in this entire story.

“Married? The rascal!” the old man thought.

“They will do it, sir,” Pen said, and opened the door.

Out stepped Mr. and Mrs. Samuel Huxter. Both knelt before the old man.

Fanny, kneeling, somehow found favour in his sight. There must have been something about her—despite Laura’s opinion.

“Will never do so again, sir,” Sam said.

“Get up, sir,” the old man said.

They rose. Fanny crept closer, tears and laughter mingling. And somehow, Mr. Huxter found himself kissing her cheek.

“What’s your name, my dear?” he asked.

“Fanny, papa,” she said.

And so it was.

A full month has passed since our last chapter, and the world has turned just enough to bring all our characters back together in the cozy little country town where we first met them—Clavering, to be precise. Time moves on, and so do people, especially when they’re involved in matters of love, money, and minor scandals.

Frederic Lightfoot, once maitre d’hôtel under Sir Francis Clavering at Clavering Park, has now taken over the Clavering Arms Hotel with all the dignity of a man who’s finally made it. His announcement reads like a royal decree:

> “The Clavering Arms, ancient and well-established, has been repaired and redecorated in the highest style of comfort. Gentlemen hunting with the Dumplingbeare hounds will find excellent stabling and loose-boxes for their horses. A commodious billiard-room has been added. The cellars are stocked with the finest wines and spirits, selected without regard to expense by C.L. Commercial gentlemen will find this hotel most agreeable. Charges have been regulated with due regard to economy.”

And indeed, the place is buzzing. The sign above the gate gleams with fresh paint. The coffee-room windows sparkle with Christmas holly. The magistrates have held court in the old Assembly card-room. The farmers’ ordinary continues as usual—only now with more customers than ever, drawn by Mrs. Lightfoot’s culinary genius. Her Indian curries and Mulligatawny soup have become local legends. Major Stokes, Captain Glanders (H.P.), and other pillars of society have publicly declared their approval, sampling her wares both privately and at the grand dinner celebrating the incorporation of the Clavering Institute’s reading room.

Sir Francis Clavering, Bart., presided over the festivities, supported by the esteemed Dr. Portman, while Barker, Esq.—the dynamic head of the ribbon factory and chief director of the Clavering and Chatteris Branch of the Great Western Railway—filled the vice-chair with great aplomb. The railway, though still under construction, is already causing excitement among engineers, laborers, and those who believe progress is inevitable—even if it means digging up half the county.

Meanwhile, the *Chatteris Champion, Clavering Agriculturist, and Baymouth Fisherman*—that noble journal of unshakable principles and unmatched loyalty to the British oak—reports that Arthur Pendennis, Esq., has abandoned his plans to run for Parliament. Rumor has it that should Sir Francis Clavering step down due to failing health, he’ll hand over his seat to a young gentleman of "colossal fortune" connected to the highest echelons of aristocracy—this same gentleman being about to marry an accomplished and lovely lady closely tied to the Clavering family.

Lady Clavering and Miss Amory have arrived at Clavering Park for the holidays. The gentry are expected. Festivities loom. And thus, the stage is set.

Now, dear reader, you may deduce what has transpired during our brief narrative pause. Though Lady Rockminster muttered slightly about Laura choosing Pendennis over Bluebeard, those who know Bluebeard’s secret understand why no other choice was possible. The kind old lady, guardian to Miss Bell, was not displeased. Marriage, after all, is the ultimate purpose of every young woman—and she saw it fulfilled. That very night, she told her maid: “Mr. Pendennis earns so much; the railroad will add to it. Miss Bell has her own fortune—perhaps more one day. For their station in life, they’ll manage handsomely.” Then, with a maternal flourish: “I shall speak to my nephew Pynsent—though I suspect he once had designs on her. But that’s long past. Not that you know anything about it, Beck. Of course not! But I’ll speak to George Pynsent, now chief secretary of the Tape and Sealing Wax Office, and see if Mr. Pendennis can’t get something decent. And you, Beck, carry my compliments to Major Pendennis—tell him I’ll call at one o’clock.”

“Of course,” muttered the old lady, “the Major must be reconciled. And he must leave his fortune to Laura’s children.”

At precisely one o’clock, Dowager Lady Rockminster arrived at Major Pendennis’s residence. The Major, though prepared for news of Pen’s broken engagement to Miss Amory, was still startled by her presence. Earlier, Pen had asked his new servant, Herr Frosch, after the Major’s health—then retreated to the coffee-room to write a note.

> “Dear Uncle,
> If there was any question between us, it’s over now. I went to Tunbridge Wells yesterday and found someone else had claimed the prize we were debating. Miss A., without a second thought for me, has given herself to Harry Foker, with his fifteen thousand a year. I walked in on their affair—found him in possession.
> And you’ll be glad to hear Tatham writes that three of my fields at Fairoaks have sold to the Railroad Company for a handsome sum. I’ll tell you more when we meet. Always your affectionate—A. P.”

When Lady Rockminster arrived, the Major greeted her with a bow fit for royalty. “I think I know what you’re about to say,” he said, smiling. “It was very kind of you to bring me the news. How well you look! How generous!”

“For the sake of his uncle,” replied the lady, with perfect politeness.

“Yes,” continued the Major, “Pen wrote me a nice note—yes, a very nice note. And I’ve learned he’s richer now. All things considered, I don’t regret the end of that business with Miss Amory. In fact, I’m rather glad.”

“We must console him,” said Lady Rockminster. “We must find him a wife.”

And then it dawned on the Major: *This* was why she’d come.

Needless to say, the conversation that followed was not recorded in full. But it ended with the marriage being approved—no objections from the Major, no resistance from Lady Rockminster, and only a few whispered intimations that carried immense weight. The Major, overwhelmed by her grace and kindness, eventually gave in. Especially when she declared, in a moment of rare passion, that Laura was far too good for Pendennis—“a paragon,” she said, “fit to be queen.”

And so, when Herr Frosch was sent upstairs to inform Miss Bell and Mr. Pendennis that the Major would receive them, Laura appeared, blushing and radiant, clinging to Pen’s arm. The Major shook their hands with genuine emotion, then bowed again to Laura—making her blush even deeper.

Happy blushes! Bright eyes shining with love! The storyteller pauses here, turns to his audience, and hopes one day they’ll all shine so.

With Pen safely out of the picture and Blanche happily betrothed to the wealthy and eager Harry Foker, joy burst forth in Lady Clavering’s household. The good Begum hadn’t felt this happy in years. She and Blanche became fast friends—almost sisters. Foker, meanwhile, was determined to shorten the mourning period that had kept him from claiming his bride. He pressed forward with wedding preparations, and Blanche, sweet and compliant, was only too willing to oblige.

Lady Clavering returned from Tunbridge. Milliners and jewellers were hired. Hymen’s chariot was being readied. Even Sir Francis benefited from the mood—so much so that reconciliation occurred between husband and wife. Sir Francis returned to London, sat at the head of his own table again, and resumed his former habits: gambling, drinking, and appearing flush with cash.

One evening, Major Pendennis and Arthur dined at Grosvenor Place. There, they encountered an old acquaintance—Mr. James Morgan—now serving as major-domo. The man, dressed in black and speaking with solemn dignity, offered them champagne with the precision of a diplomat. The Chevalier Strong was also present, in high spirits, regaling the company with tales of his adventures abroad.

“It was my Lady who invited me,” Strong whispered to Arthur. “Morgan looked like thunder when I walked in. He’s up to no good. I’ll wait for you at Hyde Park Gate.”

As Major Pendennis left, Morgan helped him with his coat and muttered something about accepting a temporary position with the Clavering family.

“I have a paper of yours, Mr. Morgan,” the Major said.

“You may show it to Sir Francis, sir, if you wish. I’m deeply grateful,” Morgan replied, eyes downcast. “If I can repay you for your kindness, I will.”

Arthur heard this—and saw the hatred in Morgan’s glance. He suddenly cried, “I forgot my handkerchief!” and dashed upstairs.

Foker was still lingering in the drawing-room. Pen gave him a meaningful look. When he returned, Blanche called out cheerfully, “Oh, Arthur—Mr. Pendennis! I want you to tell dear Laura something!”

She stepped to the door.

“What is it?” he asked, closing it.

“Have you told Harry? Do you know that villain Morgan knows everything?”

“I know,” she said.

“Have you told Harry?”

“No, no,” she said. “You won’t betray me?”

“Morgan will,” said Pen.

“No, he won’t,” said Blanche. “I promised him—n’importe. Wait until after our marriage—oh, until after our marriage—oh, how wretched I am.” The girl, who had been all smiles earlier, now seemed undone.

“Please, Blanche,” Arthur begged. “Tell him now. It’s not your fault. He’ll forgive you. Tell him tonight.”

“And give her this—Il est la—with my love, please. And if she can be at Madame Crinoline’s at half-past three, and if Lady Rockminster can spare her, I’d love to drive with her in the park.” With that, she kissed her fingers and sang back into the room, just as Morgan descended the stairs.

Pen heard Blanche’s piano play a bright tune as he rejoined his uncle.

“I told him what happened,” Arthur said.

“What was to be done?” asked the Major.

“What is to be done, begad?” the old man replied. “Leave it alone. Thank God we’re out of it. Don’t meddle, Arthur. Let them handle it.”

“I hope she tells him,” said Pen.

“She’ll take her own course,” said the Major. “Miss Amory’s a sharp one. And I’m doosid glad you’re out of it—doosid glad.”

Strong joined them later, puffing on a cigar. “I wanted to talk to you, Pendennis,” he said. “I’ve heard all the changes. Congratulations on your marriage—and on your escape. You acted like a man. You’re well out of it.”

“I went back to ask Blanche to tell Foker,” said Pen. “But I fear she won’t.”

“There’s only one policy,” said Strong.

“And lucky he who sticks to it,” said the Chevalier. “That rascal Morgan means mischief. He’s been snooping around our rooms for weeks. He found out Amory’s secret. He’s been asking questions—pumping Bolton, getting Costigan drunk. He bribed the inn porter. He got into Clavering’s service because of it. He’ll get paid handsomely—mark my words.”

“Where is Amory?” asked Pen.

“At Boulogne. I left him there. We had a terrible fight. I broke with him. He’s mad, but he’s got pluck. I had to thrash him within an inch of his life.”

“And did he challenge you?”

“Would I have shot him? No. He came to me later and said, ‘Strong, I beg your pardon. You whopped me—and you served me right.’ I shook hands. But I couldn’t live with him after that. I paid him what I owed—pawned everything. Then I played roulette with my last ten florins. If I’d lost, I’d have let him shoot me. I was tired of life. But I won. And now—I’m going into the wine business. My wife’s relatives are in Cadiz. I’ll import Spanish wine and hams. There’s a fortune in it—here’s my card.”

He handed Pen a fine card:

> **Strong & Co., Shepherd’s Inn**
> Sole agents for the celebrated Diamond Manzanilla of the Duke of Garbanzos, Grandee of Spain of the First Class
> And the famous Toboso hams, fed on acorns only in the land of Don Quixote
> Come and taste them at my chambers!

Pen laughed. “I don’t know if I’ll be allowed to go to bachelors’ parties. You know I’m going to——”

“But you must have sherry,” said Strong. “You must have sherry.”

“I’ll get it from you,” said Pen. “And I think you’re very well out of your other partnership. I hear Altamont and his daughter are corresponding.”

“Oh yes,” said Strong. “She wrote him long letters—sly little devil. He answered under cover to Mrs. Bonner. He wanted to kidnap her at first. Nothing would satisfy him but having his child back. But she didn’t want to come. He wasn’t keen either.”

The Chevalier chuckled. “Do you know why we fought? There was a widow at Baden—Madame la Baronne de la Cruche-cassee—not much better than he was. He wanted to marry her. I told her he was married. I don’t think she was much better. I saw her on the pier at Boulogne the day I returned.”

And so, we’ve reached the point the *Chatteris Champion* had already foretold.

Only a few days remained before the blissful day when Foker would claim Blanche as his own. The Clavering family had gathered to admire the most magnificent carriage in the world, standing proudly in the coach-house at the Clavering Arms. Mr. Foker’s head-coachman, ever ready to impress, showed it off in exchange for drink.

Madame Fribsby was busy making beautiful dresses for the tenants’ daughters, who would serve as bridesmaids’ chorus at the breakfast and ceremony. Festivities were planned at the Park—oxen would be slain, cups drained, bells pealed, and Sir Francis, with tears of sensibility in his eye, would bless the couple at the baronial porch.

“Ah, Mr. Huxter,” said Foker in the bar of the Clavering Arms, “a happy tenantry, the pride of the country, will gather in the hall. The ox shall be slain, the cup drained, the bells peal genteelly, and my father-in-law, with tears of emotion, shall bless us at the porch. That’s the plan, Mr. Huxter. And I hope to see you and your lovely bride beside me. What will you drink? Mrs. Lightfoot, madam, offer every refreshment to my excellent friend and body-surgeon, Mr. Samuel Huxter, M.R.C.S., and charge it to my account. And Mr. Lightfoot, sir, what will you take? Though you’ve had enough already, I think—yes, ha!”

Thus spoke Harry Foker, host and hero of the hour. He had rooms at the hotel and surrounded himself with friends. He bought drinks for everyone. He was hail-fellow-well-met. He danced around Madame Fribsby, Mrs. Lightfoot’s ally, as she sat pensive in the bar.

He consoled Mrs. Lightfoot, whose heart was heavy. For poor young Lightfoot, now in full command of the cellar, had lost control of his own desires. He drank from morning till night—tipsy, reeling through the yard and coffee-room, sharing his father’s best wines with farmers and tradesmen.

Mr. Morgan, the butler, often dropped by for a glass. He watched Lightfoot’s antics with cold disdain. Mrs. Lightfoot grew uneasy whenever Morgan was near. Just a few months married, and look at him now! Madame Fribsby sympathized. She’d known men worse. She’d suffered too. And so, perhaps, no one is truly happy. As Foker remarked, there’s bitterness in every man’s cup.

Yet none seemed to be in his. The honest young fellow was brimming with joy.

Morgan was always attentive to Foker. “Still,” said Foker candidly to Mrs. Lightfoot, “I don’t like him somehow. He looks at me like he’s measuring me for a coffin. Pa-in-law fears him. Ma-in-law’s a trump, Mrs. Lightfoot.”

“Indeed, my Lady was,” sighed Mrs. Lightfoot. “Perhaps it would have been better had I never left her.”

“No,” said Foker, “I do not like thee, Dr. Fell; the reason why I cannot tell. And he wants to be my head man. Blanche wants me to take him. Why does Miss Amory like him so?”

And so, our story ends—just before the final curtain falls.
